# Your answers. One "

## Define and classify cellular adaptation with examples.

**Definition:** Adaptations are reversible changes in the size, number, phenotype, metabolic activity or functions of cells in response to changes in their environment.

| Type | Definition | Physiologic example | Pathologic example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hypertrophy** | Increase in the size of cells, which increases the size of the organ; no new cells | Gravid uterus (smooth muscle, oestrogen); skeletal muscle of bodybuilders | Heart in pressure overload from hypertension or valvular disease |
| **Hyperplasia** | Increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue | Breast glandular epithelium at puberty and pregnancy; liver regeneration after partial hepatectomy; bone marrow red cell progenitors after bleeding or haemolysis | Endometrial hyperplasia; benign prostatic hyperplasia; papillomavirus warts |
| **Atrophy** | Reduction in the size of an organ or tissue due to a decrease in cell size and number | Notochord and thyroglossal duct in the foetus | Disuse, denervation, diminished blood supply, inadequate nutrition, loss of endocrine stimulation, pressure |
| **Metaplasia** | Reversible change in which one differentiated cell type is replaced by another | None given | Columnar to squamous in the bronchus of a smoker; squamous to columnar in Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus]; bone formation in muscle (myositis ossificans) |

## What do you mean by cellular adaptive change?

**Adaptations** are reversible functional and structural responses to changes in physiologic states (for example pregnancy) and some pathologic stimuli, during which new but altered steady states are achieved, allowing the cell to survive and continue to function.

## Write down the name of adaptive changes with an example.

| Adaptive change | Example |
|---|---|
| **Hypertrophy** | Enlargement of the heart in hypertension or valvular disease; gravid uterus |
| **Hyperplasia** | Endometrial hyperplasia from excess oestrogen; proliferation of breast glandular epithelium at puberty and pregnancy |
| **Atrophy** | Skeletal muscle in a limb immobilised in a plaster cast; atrophy of the brain in late adult life |
| **Metaplasia** | Replacement of ciliated columnar epithelium of the bronchus by squamous epithelium in a cigarette smoker |

## Define cellular adaptation.

**Adaptations** are reversible changes in the size, number, phenotype, metabolic activity, or functions of cells in response to changes in their environment.

## Examples of each type of cellular adaptation.

| Type | Physiologic example | Pathologic example |
|---|---|---|
| **Hypertrophy** | Uterus in pregnancy (smooth muscle, stimulated by oestrogen); skeletal muscle of bodybuilders | Enlargement of the heart from pressure overload, usually due to hypertension or valvular disease |
| **Hyperplasia** | Glandular epithelium of the female breast at puberty and during pregnancy; liver regeneration after donation of one lobe; bone marrow after acute bleeding or haemolysis | Endometrial hyperplasia (excess oestrogen); benign prostatic hyperplasia (androgens); skin warts and mucosal lesions due to papillomaviruses |
| **Atrophy** | Notochord and thyroglossal duct during foetal development | Disuse (limb in a plaster cast, bed rest); denervation; senile atrophy of the brain from reduced blood supply; cachexia in protein-calorie malnutrition; endometrium, vaginal epithelium and breast after menopause; prostate after castration; pressure from an enlarging benign tumour |
| **Metaplasia** | None given | Columnar to squamous in the trachea and bronchi of a smoker and in vitamin A deficiency; squamous to columnar in Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] (oesophageal squamous epithelium replaced by intestinal-like columnar cells); bone formation in muscle (myositis ossificans) |

## A 40-year-old man presented with high blood pressure for a prolonged period. What will be the effect on the myocardium? State its interpretation.

**Effect:** **hypertrophy** of the myocardium, that is, enlargement of the heart in response to pressure overload from hypertension.

**Interpretation**

- It is **pathologic hypertrophy**, an adaptation to increased workload.
- Cardiac muscle cells have only a limited capacity for division, so they respond mainly by hypertrophy.
- The cells synthesise more protein and increase the number of myofilaments per cell, so each myocyte generates more force.
- There are no new cells, only larger cells.
- Initially the hypertrophy improves function.
- Over time the adaptation often fails and sets the stage for **heart failure**.
- Beyond a limit there is degradation and loss of myofibrillar contractile elements, and in extreme cases myocyte death.

## A 70-year-old male has a blood pressure of 150/90 mm of Hg. If this condition remains untreated for years, what type of cellular adaptation will occur?

**Hypertrophy** of the myocardium (pathologic hypertrophy).

- It is caused by pressure overload from hypertension.
- Cardiac muscle cells do not divide readily, so they increase in size by synthesising more protein and more myofilaments.
- Initially it improves function, but over time it often fails and leads to heart failure.

## Describe the causes and clinical importance of hypertrophy.

**Causes**

- Increased functional demand.
- Stimulation by hormones and growth factors.
- Physiologic example: the uterus in pregnancy, stimulated by oestrogen signalling through oestrogen receptors.
- Physiologic example: skeletal muscle of bodybuilders, from increased demand.
- Pathologic example: increased workload on cardiac and skeletal muscle, most commonly pressure overload of the heart from hypertension or valvular disease.

**Clinical importance**

- Initially cardiac hypertrophy improves function.
- Over time the adaptation often fails and sets the stage for **heart failure** and other significant forms of heart disease.
- Beyond a certain limit the enlarged muscle mass cannot cope with the burden, and regressive changes occur in the myocardial fibres.
- The most important regressive change is degradation and loss of myofibrillar contractile elements, and in extreme cases myocyte death.
- It shows how an adaptation to stress can progress to significant cell injury if the stress is not relieved.
- The hypertrophied heart has increased metabolic demand, so it is more susceptible to injury.

## What is the clinical significance of pathological hyperplasia?

- Most pathologic hyperplasia is caused by excessive or inappropriate actions of hormones or growth factors on target cells.
- The process remains controlled, and it can regress or stabilise if the stimulation is eliminated.
- The increased cell division raises the risk of genetic aberrations that can drive unrestrained proliferation, so pathologic hyperplasia is a **fertile soil** in which cancer may arise.
- **Endometrial hyperplasia** is caused by an absolute or relative excess of oestrogen. It is a common cause of abnormal uterine bleeding and carries an increased risk of endometrial cancer.
- **Benign prostatic hyperplasia** is a response to androgen stimulation.
- Papillomaviruses cause hyperplastic skin warts and mucosal lesions, and some of these are precursors to cancer.

## Give the difference between hyperplasia and neoplasia.

| Feature | Hyperplasia | Neoplasia |
|---|---|---|
| **Definition** | Increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue in response to a stimulus | Disorder of cell growth triggered by acquired or, less commonly, inherited mutations |
| **Cells involved** | Mature cells and, in some cases, tissue stem cells | A single cell and its clonal progeny |
| **Driving factor** | Growth factors and hormones | Mutations that alter gene function and give the cells a growth advantage |
| **Growth control** | Remains controlled; can regress or stabilise if the stimulation is eliminated | Excessive proliferation independent of physiologic growth signals and controls |

Pathologic **hyperplasia** is distinct from cancer, but it is a fertile soil in which cancer may arise.

## Mention the important differences between hypertrophy and hyperplasia.

| Feature | Hypertrophy | Hyperplasia |
|---|---|---|
| **Definition** | Increase in the size of cells, which increases the size of the organ | Increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue in response to a stimulus |
| **New cells** | None; just larger cells, from synthesis and assembly of additional intracellular structural components | New cells are formed |
| **Cells involved** | Occurs in tissues with limited proliferative capacity; non-dividing cells such as myocardial fibres increase mass by hypertrophy only | Can take place only if the tissue contains cells capable of dividing, or abundant tissue stem cells |
| **Mechanism** | Increased cellular protein production, from growth factors and direct effects of mechanical force | Growth factor-driven proliferation of mature cells and, in some cases, increased output of new cells from tissue stem cells |
| **Physiologic example** | Gravid uterus; skeletal muscle of bodybuilders | Breast epithelium at puberty and pregnancy; liver regeneration; bone marrow |
| **Pathologic example** | Heart in hypertension or valvular disease | Endometrial hyperplasia; benign prostatic hyperplasia; papillomavirus warts |

**The two frequently** occur together and may be triggered by the same stimuli.

## Discuss how hyperplasia differs from neoplasia.

| Feature | Hyperplasia | Neoplasia |
|---|---|---|
| **Definition** | Increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue in response to a stimulus | Disorder of cell growth triggered by acquired or, less commonly, inherited mutations |
| **Cells involved** | Mature cells and, in some cases, tissue stem cells | A single cell and its clonal progeny |
| **Driving factor** | Growth factors and hormones | Mutations that alter gene function and give the cells a growth advantage |
| **Growth control** | Remains controlled; can regress or stabilise if the stimulation is eliminated | Excessive proliferation independent of physiologic growth signals and controls |

Pathologic **hyperplasia** is distinct from cancer, but it is a fertile soil in which cancer may arise.

## Define hyperplasia.

**Hyperplasia** is an increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue in response to a stimulus.

## Causes of hyperplasia.

**Physiologic hyperplasia**, due to hormones or growth factors

- Need to increase the functional capacity of hormone-sensitive organs, as in proliferation of the glandular epithelium of the female breast at puberty and during pregnancy.
- Need for a compensatory increase after damage or resection, as in liver regeneration after donation of one lobe.
- Bone marrow response to acute bleeding or premature breakdown of red cells (haemolysis): erythropoietin stimulates the growth of red cell progenitors, and red cell production can increase as much as eightfold.

**Pathologic hyperplasia**

- Excessive or inappropriate actions of hormones or growth factors acting on target cells.
- Endometrial hyperplasia: the balance between oestrogen and progesterone is disturbed, with an absolute or relative increase in oestrogen.
- Benign prostatic hyperplasia: hormonal stimulation by androgens.
- Viral infections, such as papillomaviruses, which cause skin warts and several mucosal lesions. The viruses make factors that interfere with host proteins that regulate cell proliferation.

## Clinical importance of hyperplasia.

- Pathologic hyperplasia is abnormal, but the process remains controlled, and the hyperplasia can regress or stabilise if the hormonal stimulation is eliminated.
- Endometrial hyperplasia is a common cause of abnormal uterine bleeding.
- The increased cell division elevates the risk of acquiring genetic aberrations that can drive unrestrained proliferation and give rise to cancer.
- Although hyperplasia is distinct from cancer, pathologic hyperplasia constitutes a **fertile soil** in which cancerous proliferations may eventually arise.
- Patients with hyperplasia of the endometrium are at increased risk of developing endometrial cancer.
- Some virally induced proliferations, such as papillomavirus warts and mucosal lesions, are also precursors to cancer.

## Define atrophy with examples.

Atrophy is a reduction in the size of an organ or tissue due to a decrease in cell size and number.

**Physiologic:** notochord and thyroglossal duct in foetal development.

**Pathologic**

- Disuse atrophy of skeletal muscle in a limb immobilised in a plaster cast or in bed rest.
- Denervation atrophy of muscle after nerve damage.
- Senile atrophy of the brain from reduced blood supply due to atherosclerosis.
- Muscle wasting (cachexia) in profound protein-calorie malnutrition.
- Atrophy of the endometrium, vaginal epithelium and breast after menopause; atrophy of the prostate after castration.
- Atrophy of surrounding tissue from pressure of an enlarging benign tumour.

## Why does atrophy of the brain occur in elderly people?

- In late adult life the brain may undergo progressive atrophy, mainly because of reduced blood supply.
- The reduced blood supply is a result of **atherosclerosis**, a gradual decrease in blood supply (chronic ischaemia) from slowly developing arterial occlusive disease.
- This is called **senile atrophy**.
- Loss of brain substance narrows the gyri and widens the sulci.

## What are the causes of atrophy?

**Physiologic:** common during normal development; the notochord and thyroglossal duct undergo atrophy in foetal life.

**Pathologic**

1. **Decreased workload (disuse atrophy):** immobilisation in a plaster cast or complete bed rest. Prolonged disuse also reduces the number of muscle fibres, by apoptosis, and may cause disuse osteoporosis.
2. **Loss of innervation (denervation atrophy):** damage to the nerves causes atrophy of the muscle fibres they supply.
3. **Diminished blood supply:** chronic ischaemia from slowly developing arterial occlusive disease, for example senile atrophy of the brain.
4. **Inadequate nutrition:** profound protein-calorie malnutrition (marasmus) causes muscle wasting (cachexia); cachexia is also seen in chronic inflammatory diseases and cancer, where TNF is thought to be responsible.
5. **Loss of endocrine stimulation:** loss of oestrogen after menopause causes atrophy of the endometrium, vaginal epithelium and breast; the prostate atrophies after chemical or surgical castration.
6. **Pressure:** tissue compression for any length of time, probably through ischaemia from compromised blood supply.

## Give in short the mechanism of atrophy.

- Atrophy results from decreased protein synthesis and increased protein degradation.
- Protein synthesis decreases because of reduced trophic signals, which normally enhance nutrient uptake and mRNA translation.
- Protein degradation occurs mainly by the **ubiquitin-proteasome pathway**.
- Nutrient deficiency and disuse activate ubiquitin ligases, which attach ubiquitin to cellular proteins and target them for degradation in proteasomes.
- The same pathway is thought to cause accelerated proteolysis in catabolic conditions such as cancer-related cachexia.
- There is often increased **autophagy**, with more autophagic vacuoles.
- Cell debris that resists digestion persists as residual bodies, such as lipofuscin granules, which give a brown discolouration (brown atrophy).
- The initial response is a decrease in cell size and organelles, which brings metabolic demand into balance with the lower supply and gives a new equilibrium.

## Why does pathological atrophy occur?

1. **Decreased workload** (disuse), as in a limb immobilised in a plaster cast or complete bed rest.
2. **Loss of innervation** (denervation).
3. **Diminished blood supply** (chronic ischaemia), as in senile atrophy of the brain from atherosclerosis.
4. **Inadequate nutrition**, as in profound protein-calorie malnutrition.
5. **Loss of endocrine stimulation**, as after menopause or castration.
6. **Pressure**, from tissue compression.

**The fundamental cellular change** is a decrease in cell size and organelles. This brings the cell's metabolic demands into balance with the lower levels of blood supply, nutrition or trophic stimulation, and a new equilibrium is achieved.

## Mechanism of atrophy of a cell or organ.

- Atrophy results from **decreased protein synthesis** and **increased protein degradation**.
- Protein synthesis decreases because of reduced trophic signals, such as those produced by growth receptors, which enhance uptake of nutrients and increase mRNA translation.
- Protein degradation occurs mainly by the **ubiquitin-proteasome pathway**. Nutrient deficiency and disuse may activate ubiquitin ligases, which attach ubiquitin to cellular proteins and target them for degradation in proteasomes.
- The same pathway is thought to be responsible for the accelerated proteolysis in catabolic conditions, including cancer-related cachexia.
- Atrophy is often accompanied by increased **autophagy**, with increased numbers of autophagic vacuoles.
- Cell debris that resists digestion persists in the cytoplasm as membrane-bound residual bodies, for example lipofuscin granules, which impart a brown discolouration to the tissue (brown atrophy).
- The initial response is a decrease in cell size and organelles, which may reduce the metabolic needs of the cell enough to permit its survival. In atrophic muscle the cells contain fewer mitochondria and myofilaments and a reduced amount of rough ER.
- A new equilibrium is achieved between the cell's metabolic demands and the lower levels of blood supply, nutrition or trophic stimulation.
- Early in the process, atrophic cells have diminished function but cell death is minimal.
- Atrophy caused by gradually reduced blood supply may progress to irreversible injury and cell death, often by apoptosis. Apoptosis also contributes to the atrophy of endocrine organs after hormone withdrawal.

## Define metaplasia and give two examples.

Metaplasia is a reversible change in which one differentiated cell type (epithelial or mesenchymal) is replaced by another cell type.

**Examples**

1. Columnar to squamous: replacement of the ciliated columnar epithelium of the trachea and bronchi by stratified squamous epithelium in a habitual cigarette smoker.
2. Squamous to columnar: Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] (oesophageal squamous epithelium replaced by intestinal-like columnar cells under the influence of refluxed gastric acid).

## Write about the mechanism of metaplasia and give examples.

**Mechanism**

- Metaplasia does not result from a change in the phenotype of an already differentiated cell.
- It results from reprogramming of local tissue stem cells, or from colonisation by differentiated cell populations from adjacent sites.
- The change is stimulated by signals from cytokines, growth factors and extracellular matrix components in the cells' environment.
- In stem cell reprogramming, these stimuli promote the expression of genes that drive cells toward a specific differentiation pathway.
- Vitamin A (retinoic acid) deficiency or excess can cause metaplasia, because retinoic acid regulates gene transcription directly through nuclear retinoid receptors.

**Examples**

- Columnar to squamous: bronchus of a smoker; respiratory epithelium and cornea in vitamin A deficiency; ducts of the salivary glands, pancreas or bile ducts with stones.
- Squamous to columnar: Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] (squamous epithelium of the oesophagus replaced by intestinal-like columnar cells).
- Connective tissue: bone formation in muscle after intramuscular haemorrhage (myositis ossificans).

## What are the merits and demerits of metaplasia?

**Merit**

- A cell type sensitive to a particular stress is replaced by another that is better able to withstand the adverse environment.
- Rugged stratified squamous epithelium can survive where fragile specialised columnar epithelium might succumb.

**Demerits**

- Important protective mechanisms are lost, for example mucus secretion and ciliary action of the columnar epithelium in the respiratory tract.
- Epithelial metaplasia is in most circumstances an undesirable change.
- If the predisposing influences persist, they can initiate malignant transformation in the metaplastic epithelium.
- Squamous cell carcinoma arising in squamous metaplasia of the lung is an example.
- In Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] the cancers that arise are typically gland-forming adenocarcinomas.

## Mention the clinical importance of metaplasia.

- Epithelial metaplasia in most circumstances represents an undesirable change.
- The epithelium becomes more durable, but protective mechanisms against infection, mucus secretion and ciliary action, are lost.
- Persistent influences can initiate **malignant transformation** in the metaplastic epithelium.
- Squamous cell carcinoma develops in areas of the lung where columnar epithelium has been replaced by squamous epithelium.
- In Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] the cancers that arise are typically gland-forming **adenocarcinomas**.
- Vitamin A deficiency causes squamous metaplasia of the cornea, with highly deleterious effects on vision.
- Connective tissue metaplasia, such as myositis ossificans, is not associated with increased cancer risk.

## Give four examples of squamous metaplasia.

1. **Trachea and bronchi** of a habitual cigarette smoker, where ciliated columnar cells are replaced by stratified squamous cells.
2. **Respiratory epithelium** in vitamin A (retinoic acid) deficiency.
3. **Cornea** in vitamin A deficiency.
4. **Excretory ducts** of the salivary glands, pancreas or bile ducts, in the presence of stones.

## Write down the type of metaplasia in Barrett's esophagus.

**Squamous to columnar** metaplasia: the oesophageal squamous epithelium is replaced by intestinal-like columnar cells under the influence of refluxed gastric acid.

## Why is metaplasia called a double-edged sword / epithelial metaplasia is a double-edged sword?

**Benefit**

- The cell type sensitive to the stress is replaced by one better able to withstand the adverse environment.
- Rugged stratified squamous epithelium can survive where fragile columnar epithelium might succumb.

**Price**

- Protection against infection is lost, namely mucus secretion and ciliary action of the columnar epithelium.
- If the influences that predispose to metaplasia persist, they can initiate malignant transformation.
- Squamous cell carcinoma in areas of squamous metaplasia of the lung is an example.

## Causes of metaplasia.

- **Chronic irritation of the respiratory tract:** in the habitual cigarette smoker, the ciliated columnar epithelial cells of the trachea and bronchi are often replaced by stratified squamous epithelial cells.
- **Vitamin A (retinoic acid) deficiency:** it induces squamous metaplasia in the respiratory epithelium and in the cornea. Vitamin A deficiency or excess may cause metaplasia, because retinoic acid regulates gene transcription directly through nuclear retinoid receptors.
- **Stones in the excretory ducts** of the salivary glands, pancreas or bile ducts: irritation and pressure effects lead to squamous metaplasia.
- **Refluxed gastric acid:** in Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] (oesophageal squamous epithelium replaced by intestinal-like columnar cells).
- **Intramuscular haemorrhage:** bone formation in muscle (myositis ossificans) may follow. Connective tissue metaplasia may be a result of cell or tissue injury.

## Important sites of epithelial metaplasia.

- **Trachea and bronchi** of a habitual cigarette smoker: ciliated columnar epithelium replaced by stratified squamous epithelium.
- **Respiratory epithelium and cornea** in vitamin A deficiency: squamous metaplasia.
- **Excretory ducts** of the salivary glands, pancreas or bile ducts, in the presence of stones: squamous metaplasia.
- **Oesophagus**, in Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] (oesophageal squamous epithelium replaced by intestinal-like columnar cells): squamous to columnar metaplasia.

## Classify metaplasia with examples.

| Class | Type | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Epithelial** | Columnar to squamous (the most common) | Trachea and bronchi of a smoker; respiratory epithelium and cornea in vitamin A deficiency; ducts of the salivary glands, pancreas or bile ducts with stones |
| **Epithelial** | Squamous to columnar | Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] (oesophageal squamous epithelium replaced by intestinal-like columnar cells) |
| **Connective tissue** | Formation of cartilage, bone or adipose cells in tissues that normally do not contain these elements | Bone formation in muscle after intramuscular haemorrhage (myositis ossificans) |

## Bad consequence / clinical importance of metaplasia.

- Epithelial metaplasia in most circumstances represents an undesirable change.
- The epithelium becomes more durable, but protective mechanisms against infection, mucus secretion and ciliary action, are lost.
- Persistent influences can initiate **malignant transformation** in the metaplastic epithelium.
- Squamous cell carcinoma develops in areas of the lung where columnar epithelium has been replaced by squamous epithelium.
- In Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] the cancers that arise are typically gland-forming **adenocarcinomas**.
- Vitamin A deficiency causes squamous metaplasia of the cornea, with highly deleterious effects on vision.
- Connective tissue metaplasia, such as myositis ossificans, is not associated with increased cancer risk.

## Differences between metaplasia and dysplasia.

| Feature | Metaplasia | Dysplasia |
|---|---|---|
| **Meaning** | Replacement of one cell type with another; a reversible change | Literally "disordered growth" |
| **Nature** | Adaptation to chronic injury | Premalignant change |
| **Setting** | Nearly always found in association with tissue damage, repair and regeneration | Often occurs in metaplastic epithelium, but not all metaplastic epithelium is dysplastic |
| **Cells** | The new cell type is better suited to withstand a noxious chronic environmental exposure | Considerable pleomorphism; large hyperchromatic nuclei with a high nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratio; mitotic figures increased and seen throughout the epithelium instead of only in the basal layer |
| **Architecture** | Orderly | Architectural disarray and loss of orderly differentiation |
| **Reversibility** | Reversible | With removal of inciting causes, moderately severe dysplasia may be completely reversible; progression to cancer is not inevitable |
| **Relation to cancer** | Persistent influences can initiate malignant transformation in the metaplastic epithelium | Precursor to malignant transformation; when severe and involving the full thickness of the epithelium without penetrating the basement membrane it is carcinoma in situ |
| **Examples** | Barrett oesophagus [oesophageal squamous epithelium replaced by glandular epithelium]; squamous metaplasia of bronchial epithelium in tobacco smokers | In the cervix, severe epithelial dysplasia or carcinoma in situ frequently antedates the appearance of cancer |

## Define dysplasia.

**Dysplasia** is a term that literally means "disordered growth". It is encountered principally in epithelial cells.

## What do you mean by cell injury?

**Cell injury** is the sequence of events that follows when the limits of adaptive responses are exceeded, or when cells are exposed to damaging insults, deprived of critical nutrients, or compromised by mutations that affect essential cellular functions. It is reversible up to a point, but if the injurious stimulus is persistent or severe, the cell suffers irreversible injury and ultimately undergoes cell death.

## What are the causes of cell injury?

1. **Oxygen deprivation (hypoxia):** a deficiency of oxygen that reduces aerobic oxidative respiration; causes are reduced blood flow (ischaemia), inadequate oxygenation of the blood from cardiorespiratory failure, and decreased oxygen-carrying capacity, as in anaemia, carbon monoxide poisoning and severe blood loss.
2. **Physical agents:** mechanical trauma, extremes of temperature (burns and deep cold), sudden changes in atmospheric pressure, radiation and electric shock.
3. **Chemical agents and drugs:** hypertonic glucose or salt, high concentrations of oxygen, poisons such as arsenic, cyanide and mercury, pollutants, pesticides, carbon monoxide, asbestos, alcohol, cocaine and therapeutic drugs.
4. **Infectious agents:** viruses, rickettsiae, bacteria, fungi, parasites and tapeworms.
5. **Immunologic reactions:** reactions to endogenous self-antigens cause autoimmune diseases; reactions to external agents such as viruses and environmental substances also cause injury.
6. **Genetic abnormalities:** an extra chromosome, as in Down syndrome [trisomy 21], or a single base pair substitution, as in sickle cell anaemia; they injure cells through deficient protein function, or through accumulation of damaged DNA or misfolded proteins.
7. **Nutritional imbalances:** protein-calorie deficiency, vitamin deficiencies, anorexia nervosa, and nutritional excess such as obesity and diets high in certain lipids.

## What are the types of cell injury?

1. **Reversible cell injury:** functional and structural alterations in early stages or mild forms of injury, which are correctable if the damaging stimulus is removed.
2. **Irreversible cell injury:** persistent or severe injury, in which the cell passes the point of no return and undergoes cell death, either by necrosis or by apoptosis.

## Responses of cells to injurious stimuli.

| Response | Features |
|---|---|
| **Adaptation** | Reversible functional and structural responses to changes in physiologic states and some pathologic stimuli, in which new but altered steady states are achieved, allowing the cell to survive and continue to function; hypertrophy, hyperplasia, atrophy and metaplasia |
| **Reversible cell injury** | Functional and structural alterations in early stages or mild forms of injury, which are correctable if the damaging stimulus is removed |
| **Irreversible cell injury and cell death** | Persistent or severe injury, in which the cell passes the point of no return; the two principal pathways are necrosis and apoptosis, with necroptosis, pyroptosis and ferroptosis as additional pathways |
| **Other changes** | Intracellular accumulations from metabolic derangements and chronic injury; pathologic calcification at sites of cell death; cellular ageing |

- Adaptation, reversible injury and cell death represent stages of progressive impairment following different types of insults, but they do not always occur sequentially.
- The response depends on the nature of the injury, its duration and its severity.
- The consequences depend on the type, state and adaptability of the injured cell.

## Mention the types of reversible cell injury.

1. **Cellular swelling:** the earliest manifestation of almost all forms of cell injury; it results from influx of water.
2. **Fatty change:** occurs in organs actively involved in lipid metabolism, such as the liver; toxic injury disrupts metabolic pathways and leads to rapid accumulation of triglyceride-filled lipid vacuoles.

## Write down the morphological types of cell injury.

**Reversible injury**

- Cellular swelling (hydropic change or vacuolar degeneration).
- Fatty change.

**Irreversible injury (cell death)**

- Necrosis.
- Apoptosis.

## Write down the morphological types of irreversible cell injury.

1. **Necrosis**, which shows six tissue patterns:

- Coagulative.
- Liquefactive.
- Gangrenous.
- Caseous.
- Fat necrosis.
- Fibrinoid.

2. **Apoptosis.**

## Mention the morphological pattern of cell death.

There are two principal types of cell death, **necrosis** and **apoptosis**.

**Patterns of tissue necrosis**

- Coagulative.
- Liquefactive.
- Gangrenous.
- Caseous.
- Fat necrosis.
- Fibrinoid.

**Other forms of cell death**

- **Necroptosis:** resembles necrosis morphologically but is genetically controlled, like apoptosis.
- **Pyroptosis:** lytic death of microbe-infected cells with release of inflammatory mediators such as IL-1.
- **Ferroptosis:** iron-dependent cell death induced by lipid peroxidation.

## What are the morphological changes in reversible cell injury?

**Light microscopy**

- Cellular swelling, which causes pallor, increased turgor and increased weight of the organ.
- Small clear vacuoles in the cytoplasm, which are distended and pinched-off segments of the ER; this is called hydropic change or vacuolar degeneration.
- Eosinophilia of the cytoplasm on H&E because of loss of RNA; it becomes more pronounced as injury progresses toward necrosis.
- Fatty change in organs involved in lipid metabolism, such as the liver.

**Electron microscopy**

1. Plasma membrane alterations: blebbing, blunting and loss of microvilli.
2. Mitochondrial swelling, with small amorphous densities.
3. Myelin figures in the cytosol, composed of phospholipids from damaged cellular membranes.
4. Dilation of the ER, with detachment of polysomes.
5. Nuclear alterations, with disaggregation of granular and fibrillar elements.

## Features of reversible cell injury.

**Light microscopy**

- Cellular swelling, which causes pallor, increased turgor and increased weight of the organ.
- Small clear vacuoles in the cytoplasm, which are distended and pinched-off segments of the ER; this is called hydropic change or vacuolar degeneration.
- Eosinophilia of the cytoplasm on H&E because of loss of RNA; it becomes more pronounced as injury progresses toward necrosis.
- Fatty change in organs involved in lipid metabolism, such as the liver.

**Electron microscopy**

1. Plasma membrane alterations: blebbing, blunting and loss of microvilli.
2. Mitochondrial swelling, with small amorphous densities.
3. Myelin figures in the cytosol, composed of phospholipids from damaged cellular membranes.
4. Dilation of the ER, with detachment of polysomes.
5. Nuclear alterations, with disaggregation of granular and fibrillar elements.

## Changes that occur in a cell in cellular swelling.

**How it develops**

1. Oxygen deficiency interferes with mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation, or the mitochondria are damaged by radiation or toxins, and ATP is depleted.
2. The ATP-dependent sodium-potassium pump of the plasma membrane fails.
3. Sodium enters and accumulates inside the cell, and the potassium concentration falls.
4. The net solute gain results in osmotically driven water accumulation, which leads to cell swelling and dilation of the ER.

**Changes seen**

- Cellular swelling is the earliest manifestation of almost all forms of injury to cells.
- When it affects many cells it causes pallor, increased turgor and increased weight of the affected organ.
- Small clear vacuoles appear within the cytoplasm. They are distended and pinched-off segments of the ER, and the pattern is called **hydropic change** or **vacuolar degeneration**.
- The cytoplasm appears red (eosinophilic) on H&E because of loss of RNA.
- There is generalised swelling of the cell and its organelles, blebbing of the plasma membrane, detachment of ribosomes from the ER and clumping of nuclear chromatin.
- The change is reversible, but in myocardial ischaemia it may progress to irreversibility within 1 or 2 hours.

## Features of irreversible cell injury.

- Persistent or excessive injury causes cells to pass the "point of no return" into irreversible injury and cell death. This point is still largely undefined.

**Two phenomena consistently characterise irreversibility**

1. Inability to reverse mitochondrial dysfunction, that is lack of oxidative phosphorylation and ATP generation, even after resolution of the original injury.
2. Profound disturbances in membrane function. Injury to lysosomal membranes results in the enzymatic dissolution of the cell that is characteristic of necrosis.

**Morphology**

- Eosinophilia.
- Nuclear shrinkage, fragmentation and dissolution.
- Breakdown of plasma membrane and organellar membranes.
- Abundant myelin figures.
- Leakage and enzymatic digestion of cellular contents.
- Unmistakable light microscopic evidence of cell death may not be seen until 4 to 12 hours after onset of ischaemia.

**Cell death**

- Different injurious stimuli induce death mainly by necrosis and apoptosis.

## Morphological changes that occur in a necrosed cell.

**Cytoplasmic changes**

- Increased eosinophilia on H&E, from loss of cytoplasmic RNA and accumulation of denatured cytoplasmic proteins that bind eosin.
- Glassy homogeneous appearance, mainly from loss of glycogen particles.
- Vacuolated, moth-eaten cytoplasm once enzymes have digested the organelles.
- Myelin figures, which are large whorled phospholipid precipitates; they are phagocytosed or degraded to fatty acids, which may calcify.
- On electron microscopy: discontinuities in plasma and organelle membranes, marked dilation of mitochondria with large amorphous densities, intracytoplasmic myelin figures, amorphous debris, and aggregates of fluffy material representing denatured protein.

**Nuclear changes**

1. **Karyolysis:** fading of chromatin basophilia, from enzymatic degradation of DNA by endonucleases.
2. **Pyknosis:** nuclear shrinkage with increased basophilia; the chromatin condenses into a dense, shrunken basophilic mass.
3. **Karyorrhexis:** fragmentation of the pyknotic nucleus.

After 1 or 2 days the nucleus of the necrotic cell disappears totally.

## Describe in brief the sequence of events in ischemic injury.

1. **Intracellular oxygen tension falls**, oxidative phosphorylation fails and ATP generation decreases.
2. **A fall** in ATP with a rise in AMP stimulates phosphorylase and phosphofructokinase, which increase glycogenolysis and glycolysis; glycogen stores are rapidly depleted.
3. **Anaerobic glycolysis produces** lactic acid and inorganic phosphates, which lower the intracellular pH and reduce the activity of many cytosolic enzymes.
4. **The sodium-potassium ATPase** of the plasma membrane fails. Sodium and water enter the cell and potassium falls, causing cell and ER swelling. This stage is reversible.
5. **With prolonged ATP depletion**, ribosomes detach from the rough ER and polysomes dissociate, so protein synthesis falls, and protein misfolding may increase.
6. **The mitochondrial permeability** transition pore opens, membrane potential is lost and ATP depletion progresses.
7. **Severe or prolonged** ischaemia causes severe mitochondrial swelling, calcium influx into mitochondria and the cell, and rupture of lysosomes and the plasma membrane.
8. **The cell dies** by necrosis, and by apoptosis through release of cytochrome c from mitochondria.

## Write down the mechanism of irreversible cell injury.

- **The point** of no return, at which damage becomes irreversible, is still largely undefined.
- **Two phenomena consistently** characterise irreversibility.

1. **Inability** to reverse mitochondrial dysfunction, that is lack of oxidative phosphorylation and ATP generation, even after the original injury resolves.
2. **Profound disturbances** in membrane function.

- **Mitochondrial damage opens** the permeability transition pore, with loss of membrane potential and progressive ATP depletion that culminates in necrosis.
- **Injury** to lysosomal membranes lets lysosomal enzymes enter the cytoplasm and digest the cell, which is the enzymatic dissolution characteristic of necrosis.
- **Severe mitochondrial damage** with ATP depletion, and rupture of lysosomal and plasma membranes, are typically associated with necrosis.

## How is depletion of ATP associated with hypoxic cell injury?

- ATP is produced mainly by oxidative phosphorylation in the mitochondria, in which oxygen is reduced by the electron transport system. In far smaller amounts, ATP is also produced by glycolysis in the absence of oxygen.
- ATP is required for virtually all synthetic and degradative processes within the cell, including membrane transport, protein synthesis, lipogenesis and phospholipid turnover. Depletion of ATP to 5% to 10% of normal levels has widespread effects on many critical cellular systems.

**Sequence in hypoxic injury**

1. Reduced supply of oxygen and nutrients, as in ischaemia and hypoxia, stops oxidative phosphorylation. ATP decreases and adenosine monophosphate increases.
2. These changes stimulate phosphorylase and phosphofructokinase, which increase glycogenolysis and glycolysis. Glycogen stores are rapidly depleted.
3. Anaerobic glycolysis leads to accumulation of lactic acid and inorganic phosphates. This reduces the intracellular pH and decreases the activity of many cytosolic enzymes.
4. The sodium-potassium pump of the plasma membrane (Na+,K+-ATPase) is reduced. Sodium enters and accumulates inside the cell and potassium falls. Osmotically driven water accumulation leads to cell swelling and ER dilation.
5. With prolonged ATP depletion, ribosomes detach from the rough ER and polysomes dissociate, so protein synthesis is reduced. Protein misfolding may increase.
6. Mitochondrial damage opens the mitochondrial permeability transition pore, with loss of mitochondrial membrane potential, failure of oxidative phosphorylation and progressive ATP depletion that culminates in necrosis.
7. Incomplete oxidative phosphorylation also leads to the formation of reactive oxygen species.
8. Ultimately there is irreversible damage to mitochondrial and lysosomal membranes, and the cell undergoes necrosis.

## Names of free radicals.

- **Superoxide anion:** formed by transfer of one electron to oxygen.
- **Hydrogen peroxide:** formed by transfer of two electrons.
- **Hydroxyl radical:** formed by transfer of three electrons. It is the most reactive oxygen-derived free radical.
- **Peroxynitrite anion:** produced by interaction of the superoxide anion and nitric oxide.
- **Nitric oxide:** can act as a free radical.
- The **CCl3 radical**, generated from carbon tetrachloride by enzymatic metabolism, is a free radical that is not a reactive oxygen species.

## Name the antioxidants.

**Antioxidants that block free radical formation or scavenge free radicals**

- Vitamins E and A, which are lipid-soluble.
- Ascorbic acid.
- Glutathione in the cytosol.

**Storage and transport proteins that bind iron and copper**

- Transferrin, ferritin, lactoferrin and ceruloplasmin.

**Free radical-scavenging enzymes**

- **Catalase**, present in peroxisomes, decomposes hydrogen peroxide.
- **Superoxide dismutases (SODs)** convert the superoxide anion to hydrogen peroxide. Manganese-SOD is localised in mitochondria and copper-zinc-SOD is found in the cytoplasm.
- **Glutathione peroxidase** catalyses free radical breakdown.

## Enzymes activated by increased intracellular calcium.

- **Phospholipases:** cause membrane damage.
- **Proteases:** break down both membrane and cytoskeletal proteins.
- **Endonucleases:** responsible for DNA and chromatin fragmentation.
- **ATPases:** hasten ATP depletion.

## Discuss the role of calcium in cell injury.

**Normal state**

- Calcium ions normally serve as second messengers in several signalling pathways.
- Cytosolic free calcium is kept at very low concentration, less than 0.1 μmol, compared with extracellular levels of 1.3 mmol.
- Most intracellular calcium is sequestered in mitochondria and the ER.

**In injury**

- Ischaemia and certain toxins cause an excessive increase in cytosolic calcium.
- Calcium depletion protects cultured cells from injury by a variety of harmful stimuli.
- Accumulation of calcium in mitochondria opens the mitochondrial permeability transition pore, with failure of ATP generation.
- Increased cytosolic calcium activates phospholipases, proteases, endonucleases and ATPases.
- Calcium influx may also trigger apoptosis.

## Discuss the sources and possible consequences of increased cytosolic calcium in cell injury.

**Sources of increased cytosolic calcium**

- Initially, release from intracellular stores, which are the mitochondria and the ER.
- Later, increased influx across the plasma membrane.

**Consequences**

- Accumulation of calcium in mitochondria opens the mitochondrial permeability transition pore, with failure of ATP generation.
- Activation of **phospholipases**, which cause membrane damage.
- Activation of **proteases**, which break down membrane and cytoskeletal proteins.
- Activation of **endonucleases**, which cause DNA and chromatin fragmentation.
- Activation of **ATPases**, which hastens ATP depletion.
- Calcium influx may also trigger apoptosis.

## How are free radicals initiated?

1. **Normal redox reactions of metabolism:** during respiration, oxygen is reduced by four electrons to give two water molecules. Partially reduced intermediates form in small amounts: superoxide anion (one electron), hydrogen peroxide (two electrons) and hydroxyl radical (three electrons).
2. **Absorption of radiant energy:** for example ultraviolet light and X-rays; ionising radiation can hydrolyse water into hydroxyl and hydrogen free radicals.
3. **Bursts of ROS in activated leucocytes during inflammation:** produced by the plasma membrane complex NADPH oxidase; some intracellular oxidases such as xanthine oxidase also generate superoxide anion. Defective leucocyte superoxide production causes chronic granulomatous disease.
4. **Enzymatic metabolism of exogenous chemicals or drugs:** for example carbon tetrachloride generates the CCl3 radical.
5. **Transition metals such as iron and copper:** they donate or accept free electrons and catalyse free radical formation, as in the Fenton reaction [iron-catalysed generation of hydroxyl radicals from hydrogen peroxide] (hydrogen peroxide plus ferrous iron gives ferric iron, hydroxyl radical and hydroxide).
6. **Nitric oxide:** generated by endothelial cells, macrophages, neurons and other cells, it can act as a free radical and can be converted to the highly reactive peroxynitrite anion, nitrogen dioxide and nitrite.

## What are the effects of free radicals on cells?

1. **Lipid peroxidation in membranes:** free radicals attack the double bonds of unsaturated fatty acids in plasma and organellar membranes. This yields unstable peroxides, and an autocatalytic chain reaction (propagation) causes extensive membrane damage.
2. **Oxidative modification of proteins:** oxidation of amino acid side chains, formation of covalent protein-protein cross-links such as disulphide bonds, and oxidation of the protein backbone. This can damage enzyme active sites, disrupt the shape of structural proteins and enhance proteasomal degradation of misfolded proteins.
3. **Lesions in DNA:** single- and double-strand breaks, cross-linking of strands and formation of adducts. Oxidative DNA damage is implicated in cell ageing and in malignant transformation.
4. Free radicals can also trigger **apoptosis**, as well as necrosis.

## Write short note on: Free radical

**Definition:** free radicals are chemical species that have a single unpaired electron in an outer orbit. They attack and modify adjacent molecules such as proteins, lipids, carbohydrates and nucleic acids. Some reactions are autocatalytic, which propagates the chain of damage.

**Generation**

- Normal redox reactions of metabolism.
- Absorption of radiant energy.
- Bursts of ROS in activated leucocytes.
- Metabolism of exogenous chemicals or drugs.
- Transition metals such as iron and copper.
- Nitric oxide.

**Principal reactive oxygen species**

- Superoxide anion, hydrogen peroxide, hydroxyl radical and peroxynitrite.
- The hydroxyl radical is the most reactive oxygen-derived free radical and is the principal ROS damaging lipids, proteins and DNA.

**Removal**

- Antioxidants block free radical formation or scavenge free radicals: vitamins E and A, ascorbic acid and glutathione.
- Iron and copper are bound to storage and transport proteins (transferrin, ferritin, lactoferrin, ceruloplasmin).
- **Catalase** in peroxisomes decomposes hydrogen peroxide.
- **Superoxide dismutase** converts superoxide anion to hydrogen peroxide; manganese-SOD is in mitochondria and copper-zinc-SOD is in the cytoplasm.
- **Glutathione peroxidase** breaks down free radicals; the ratio of oxidised to reduced glutathione reflects the oxidative state of the cell.

**Effects**

- Lipid peroxidation of membranes.
- Oxidative modification of proteins.
- Lesions in DNA.
- Triggering of apoptosis.

**Clinical relevance**

- Oxidative stress, meaning excess free radicals from increased production or decreased scavenging, is implicated in chemical and radiation injury, ischaemia-reperfusion injury, cellular ageing and microbial killing by phagocytes.
- It is also implicated in cancer and degenerative diseases such as Alzheimer disease [progressive dementia with amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles].

## Describe the biochemical mechanism leading to cell membrane damage.

Early loss of selective membrane permeability, leading to overt membrane damage, is a consistent feature of most forms of cell injury except apoptosis. In ischaemic cells it results from ATP depletion and calcium-mediated activation of phospholipases. The plasma membrane can also be damaged directly by bacterial toxins, viral proteins, lytic complement components and physical and chemical agents.

1. **Reactive oxygen species:** oxygen free radicals injure membranes by lipid peroxidation.
2. **Decreased phospholipid synthesis:** defective mitochondrial function or hypoxia reduces ATP production and thus the energy-dependent synthesis of phospholipids, which affects all cellular membranes including those of mitochondria.
3. **Increased phospholipid breakdown:** raised cytosolic and mitochondrial calcium activates calcium-dependent phospholipases. The breakdown products are unesterified free fatty acids, acyl carnitine and lysophospholipids, which have a detergent effect on membranes and may change permeability.
4. **Cytoskeletal abnormalities:** proteases activated by cytosolic calcium damage the filaments that anchor the plasma membrane to the cell interior. With cell swelling, especially in myocardial cells, the membrane detaches from the cytoskeleton and is susceptible to stretching and rupture.

## What is reperfusion injury?

Restoration of blood flow to ischaemic tissues can promote recovery of reversibly injured cells, but it can also paradoxically exacerbate cell injury and cause cell death. Reperfused tissues may lose viable cells in addition to those irreversibly damaged by the ischaemia; this process is called ischaemia-**reperfusion injury**.

## Discuss the mechanism of reperfusion injury.

New damaging processes are set in motion during reperfusion, which kill cells that might otherwise have recovered.

1. **Oxidative stress:** reoxygenation increases generation of reactive oxygen and nitrogen species, from incomplete reduction of oxygen in leucocytes and in damaged endothelial and parenchymal cells. Antioxidant defences compromised during ischaemia sensitise the cells to free radical damage.
2. **Intracellular calcium overload:** it begins during acute ischaemia and is worsened during reperfusion by calcium influx through damaged cell membranes and ROS-mediated injury to the sarcoplasmic reticulum. It favours opening of the mitochondrial permeability transition pore, with ATP depletion and further injury.
3. **Inflammation:** danger signals from dead cells, cytokines from resident macrophages and increased adhesion molecules on hypoxic parenchymal and endothelial cells recruit neutrophils, which cause additional tissue injury.
4. **Complement activation:** some IgM antibodies deposit in ischaemic tissues; when blood flow resumes, complement binds to them and is activated, which worsens cell injury and inflammation.

## Why does inflammation occur in necrosis?

- Severe membrane damage lets cellular contents leak through the damaged plasma membrane into the extracellular space, where they elicit a host reaction, which is inflammation.
- Some substances released from injured cells are called **damage-associated molecular patterns (DAMPs)**: ATP from damaged mitochondria, uric acid (a breakdown product of DNA), and many other molecules normally confined within healthy cells.
- Receptors on macrophages and most other cell types recognise these molecules.
- This triggers phagocytosis of the debris and production of cytokines that induce inflammation.
- Inflammatory cells produce more proteolytic enzymes, and phagocytosis with enzymatic digestion usually clears the necrotic cells.

## Define necrosis.

Necrosis is a pathologic process in which **cellular membranes are destroyed**, enzymes and other constituents leak out, and **local inflammation** is induced to clear the damaged cells.
## Give the cytoplasmic and nuclear morphological changes of a necrosed cell.

**Cytoplasmic changes**

- Increased eosinophilia on H&E, from loss of cytoplasmic RNA and accumulation of denatured cytoplasmic proteins that bind eosin.
- Glassy homogeneous appearance, mainly from loss of glycogen particles.
- Vacuolated, moth-eaten cytoplasm once enzymes have digested the organelles.
- Myelin figures, which are large whorled phospholipid precipitates; they are phagocytosed or degraded to fatty acids, which may calcify.
- On electron microscopy: discontinuities in plasma and organelle membranes, marked dilation of mitochondria with large amorphous densities, intracytoplasmic myelin figures, amorphous debris, and aggregates of fluffy material representing denatured protein.

**Nuclear changes**

1. **Karyolysis:** fading of chromatin basophilia, from enzymatic degradation of DNA by endonucleases.
2. **Pyknosis:** nuclear shrinkage with increased basophilia; the chromatin condenses into a dense, shrunken basophilic mass.
3. **Karyorrhexis:** fragmentation of the pyknotic nucleus.

After 1 or 2 days the nucleus of the necrotic cell disappears totally.

## Mention the basic types of necrosis with examples.

| Type | Example |
|---|---|
| **Coagulative** | Infarct, for example wedge-shaped infarct of the kidney, from vessel obstruction in any organ except the brain |
| **Liquefactive** | Focal bacterial or occasionally fungal infection with pus; hypoxic infarct of the brain |
| **Gangrenous** | Lower leg that has lost its blood supply; wet gangrene when bacterial infection is added |
| **Caseous** | Tuberculosis of the lung |
| **Fat necrosis** | Acute pancreatitis, with chalky-white fat saponification in the peritoneum and mesentery |
| **Fibrinoid** | Immune complex deposition in the walls of arteries |

## Describe the morphological types of necrosis.

Necrosis of tissues has several morphologically distinct patterns, which give clues to the underlying cause.

1. **Coagulative necrosis:** architecture of the dead tissue is preserved for at least some days and the tissue is firm. Injury denatures structural proteins and enzymes, which blocks proteolysis. Intensely eosinophilic cells with indistinct or reddish nuclei persist for days or weeks. It follows ischaemia from vessel obstruction in all organs except the brain, and a localised area is an infarct.
2. **Liquefactive necrosis:** digestion of the dead cells turns the tissue into a viscous liquid. It occurs in focal bacterial or occasionally fungal infections, where microbes attract leucocytes that liberate enzymes; the creamy yellow material is pus. Hypoxic death of cells in the central nervous system is often liquefactive.
3. **Gangrenous necrosis:** not a specific pattern, but a clinical term for a limb, generally the lower leg, that has lost its blood supply and shows necrosis, typically coagulative, involving multiple tissue planes. Added bacterial infection increases liquefaction and gives wet gangrene.
4. **Caseous necrosis:** seen most often in tuberculous infection. The area is friable, white and cheese-like. Microscopically it is a structureless collection of fragmented or lysed cells and amorphous granular debris, enclosed within epithelioid macrophages, which is a granuloma.
5. **Fat necrosis:** focal destruction of fat from release of activated pancreatic lipases in acute pancreatitis. Enzymes liquefy fat cell membranes, triglycerides are split, and the fatty acids combine with calcium to form chalky-white areas (fat saponification). Histology shows shadowy outlines of necrotic fat cells, basophilic calcium deposits and inflammation.
6. **Fibrinoid necrosis:** special form of vascular damage in immune reactions. Antigen-antibody complexes deposited in arterial walls, with leaked plasma proteins, give a bright pink amorphous appearance on H&E.

## Describe the naked eye features of different types of necrosis.

| Type | Naked eye features |
|---|---|
| **Coagulative** | Firm texture; kidney infarct is wedge-shaped and yellow |
| **Liquefactive** | Tissue transformed into a viscous liquid; pus is creamy yellow; brain infarct shows dissolution of tissue |
| **Gangrenous** | Necrosis of a limb, generally the lower leg, involving multiple tissue planes; liquefaction in wet gangrene |
| **Caseous** | Friable, white and cheese-like; yellow-white cheesy debris in tuberculous lung |
| **Fat necrosis** | Grossly visible chalky-white areas of fat saponification, for example in the mesentery |
| **Fibrinoid** | No gross feature is described; on H&E it is a bright pink amorphous area in the arterial wall |

## How will you differentiate between liquefactive and coagulative necrosis?

| Feature | Coagulative necrosis | Liquefactive necrosis |
|---|---|---|
| **Tissue change** | Architecture of the dead tissue is preserved for at least some days; firm texture | Dead cells are digested and the tissue becomes a viscous liquid |
| **Mechanism** | Injury denatures structural proteins and enzymes, which blocks proteolysis | Microbes attract leucocytes, which liberate enzymes that digest the cells |
| **Appearance** | Intensely eosinophilic cells with indistinct or reddish nuclei; wedge-shaped yellow kidney infarct | Creamy yellow pus; brain infarct shows dissolution of tissue |
| **Setting** | Ischaemia from vessel obstruction in all organs except the brain; localised area is an infarct | Focal bacterial or occasionally fungal infections; hypoxic death of cells in the central nervous system |

## Classify necrosis.

| Type | Features | Typical setting |
|---|---|---|
| **Coagulative** | Architecture of the dead tissue is preserved for at least some days; firm texture; injury denatures structural proteins and enzymes, which blocks proteolysis | Ischaemia from vessel obstruction, in all organs except the brain; a localised area is called an **infarct** |
| **Liquefactive** | Digestion of the dead cells; tissue becomes a viscous liquid; necrotic material is creamy yellow and is called **pus** | Focal bacterial or, occasionally, fungal infections; hypoxic death of cells in the central nervous system |
| **Gangrenous** | Not a specific pattern of cell death; clinical term; necrosis, typically coagulative, involving multiple tissue planes; added bacterial infection gives more liquefactive necrosis, called **wet gangrene** | Limb, generally the lower leg, that has lost its blood supply |
| **Caseous** | Friable, white, cheese-like; structureless collection of fragmented or lysed cells and amorphous granular debris, enclosed within epithelioid macrophages (a **granuloma**) | Tuberculous infection |
| **Fat necrosis** | Focal destruction of fat by activated pancreatic lipases; released fatty acids combine with calcium to give chalky-white areas (**fat saponification**); histology shows shadowy outlines of necrotic fat cells, basophilic calcium deposits and inflammation | Acute pancreatitis |
| **Fibrinoid** | Special form of vascular damage; antigen-antibody complexes deposited in arterial walls, with leaked plasma proteins, give a bright pink amorphous appearance on H&E | Immune reactions involving blood vessels |
## Examples of each type of necrosis.

| Type | Example |
|---|---|
| **Coagulative** | Infarct, for example wedge-shaped infarct of the kidney, from vessel obstruction in any organ except the brain |
| **Liquefactive** | Focal bacterial or occasionally fungal infection with pus; hypoxic infarct of the brain |
| **Gangrenous** | Lower leg that has lost its blood supply; wet gangrene when bacterial infection is added |
| **Caseous** | Tuberculosis of the lung |
| **Fat necrosis** | Acute pancreatitis, with chalky-white fat saponification in the peritoneum and mesentery |
| **Fibrinoid** | Immune complex deposition in the walls of arteries |

## Morphology of coagulative necrosis.

**Gross**

- Firm texture.
- Architecture of the dead tissue is preserved for at least some days.
- Kidney infarct is wedge-shaped and yellow.

**Microscopy**

- Intensely **eosinophilic** cells with indistinct or reddish nuclei persist for days or weeks.
- Preserved cellular outlines with **loss of nuclei**.
- Inflammatory infiltrate at the edge of the infarct.

**Mechanism of persistence**

- Injury denatures structural proteins and also enzymes, which blocks proteolysis of the dead cells.

**Fate**

- Necrotic cells are broken down by lysosomal enzymes from infiltrating leucocytes.
- The same leucocytes remove the debris by phagocytosis.
## Example of coagulative necrosis.

- The classic example is an **infarct**, a localised area of coagulative necrosis.
- Ischaemia caused by obstruction in a vessel may lead to coagulative necrosis of the supplied tissue in all organs except the brain.
- A wedge-shaped, yellow kidney infarct is an example.
- A myocardial infarct is necrosis of a portion of the heart caused by death of many myocardial cells.

## Common examples of liquefactive necrosis.

- Focal bacterial or, occasionally, fungal infections. Microbes stimulate the accumulation of leucocytes and the liberation of enzymes from these cells. The necrotic material is frequently creamy yellow because of the leucocytes and is called **pus**.
- Hypoxic death of cells within the central nervous system, for example an infarct in the brain showing dissolution of the tissue. The reason is unknown.
- Wet gangrene: when bacterial infection is superimposed on gangrenous necrosis, there is increased liquefactive necrosis because of the degradative enzymes in the bacteria and the attracted leucocytes.

## Differences between coagulative and caseous necrosis.

| Feature | Coagulative necrosis | Caseous necrosis |
|---|---|---|
| **Architecture** | Architecture of the dead tissue is preserved for at least some days | Structureless collection of fragmented or lysed cells and amorphous granular debris |
| **Texture and colour** | Firm; kidney infarct is wedge-shaped and yellow | Friable and white, cheese-like; yellow-white cheesy debris in tuberculosis of the lung |
| **Microscopy** | Intensely eosinophilic cells with indistinct or reddish nuclei persist for days or weeks | Debris enclosed within a distinctive collection of macrophages called epithelioid cells, which is characteristic of a granuloma |
| **Mechanism** | Injury denatures structural proteins and enzymes, which blocks proteolysis of the dead cells | Not given |
| **Setting** | Ischaemia from obstruction of a vessel, in all organs except the brain; a localised area is an infarct | Encountered most often in foci of tuberculous infection |

## What do you mean by gangrenous necrosis?

**Gangrenous necrosis** is not a specific pattern of cell death, but the term is commonly used in clinical practice. It is usually applied to a limb, generally the lower leg, that has lost its blood supply and has undergone necrosis, typically coagulative necrosis, involving multiple tissue planes.

## What is ischemic necrosis?

Ischaemia caused by obstruction in a vessel leads to coagulative **necrosis** of the supplied tissue in all organs except the brain. A localised area of coagulative necrosis is called an infarct.

In the central nervous system, hypoxic death of cells often shows liquefactive **necrosis**.

## How do coagulative necrosis and liquefactive necrosis differ?

| Feature | Coagulative necrosis | Liquefactive necrosis |
|---|---|---|
| **Tissue change** | Architecture of the dead tissue is preserved for at least some days; firm texture | Dead cells are digested and the tissue becomes a viscous liquid |
| **Mechanism** | Injury denatures structural proteins and enzymes, which blocks proteolysis | Microbes attract leucocytes, which liberate enzymes that digest the cells |
| **Appearance** | Intensely eosinophilic cells with indistinct or reddish nuclei; wedge-shaped yellow kidney infarct | Creamy yellow pus; brain infarct shows dissolution of tissue |
| **Setting** | Ischaemia from vessel obstruction in all organs except the brain; localised area is an infarct | Focal bacterial or occasionally fungal infections; hypoxic death of cells in the central nervous system |

## What are the morphological differences between coagulation necrosis and liquefactive necrosis?

| Feature | Coagulative necrosis | Liquefactive necrosis |
|---|---|---|
| **Tissue change** | Architecture of the dead tissue is preserved for at least some days; firm texture | Dead cells are digested and the tissue becomes a viscous liquid |
| **Mechanism** | Injury denatures structural proteins and enzymes, which blocks proteolysis | Microbes attract leucocytes, which liberate enzymes that digest the cells |
| **Appearance** | Intensely eosinophilic cells with indistinct or reddish nuclei; wedge-shaped yellow kidney infarct | Creamy yellow pus; brain infarct shows dissolution of tissue |
| **Setting** | Ischaemia from vessel obstruction in all organs except the brain; localised area is an infarct | Focal bacterial or occasionally fungal infections; hypoxic death of cells in the central nervous system |

## Give the morphology of caseation necrosis.

**Naked eye**

- Friable, white, cheese-like area; the word caseous means cheese-like.
- Yellow-white cheesy debris, seen for example in tuberculosis of the lung.

**Microscopy**

- Structureless collection of fragmented or lysed cells and amorphous granular debris.
- It is enclosed within a distinctive collection of macrophages called epithelioid cells.
- This appearance is characteristic of a **granuloma**.

**Setting**

- Seen most often in foci of tuberculous infection.

## A 50-year-old, chronic alcoholic man was admitted with central acute abdominal pain radiating to the back. His serum amylase was 12000 U/L (normal < 300 U/L). What will be your provisional diagnosis?

**Acute pancreatitis**, with alcohol as the likely cause.

- Biliary tract disease and alcoholism account for approximately 80% of cases of acute pancreatitis.
- Abdominal pain is the cardinal manifestation. It is constant, intense and referred to the upper or middle back.
- Elevated plasma levels of amylase and lipase, released from the damaged pancreatic acinar cells, support the diagnosis.

## What type of necrosis will be observed in compression/peritoneum in acute pancreatitis?

**Fat necrosis.**

- Activated pancreatic lipases are released into the substance of the pancreas and the peritoneal cavity, causing focal areas of fat destruction.
- Fat saponification produces grossly visible **chalky-white areas**, for example in the mesentery.
- On histology the necrotic areas show shadowy outlines of necrotic fat cells, basophilic calcium deposits and an inflammatory reaction.

## Why does fat necrosis occur in acute pancreatitis?

1. Pancreatic enzymes leak out of damaged acinar cells.
2. They liquefy the membranes of fat cells in the peritoneum.
3. Triglyceride esters are released from the fat cells and are split by pancreatic lipases.
4. Fatty acids are generated that combine with calcium to produce grossly visible chalky-white areas, called **fat saponification**.

## What is the significance of apoptosis?

Apoptosis is a regulated mechanism of cell death that serves to eliminate unwanted and irreparably damaged cells, with the least possible host reaction.

**Physiologic significance**

- It eliminates cells that are no longer needed, such as supernumerary cells during development, and is required for normal embryogenesis.
- It maintains a constant number of cells in tissues. Humans turn over almost 1 million cells per second.
- It brings about involution of hormone-dependent tissues on hormone withdrawal.
- It eliminates lymphocytes that fail to produce functional antigen receptors and potentially harmful self-reactive lymphocytes, which prevents immune reactions against one's own tissues.
- It removes host cells that have served their useful purpose, such as neutrophils in an acute inflammatory response.

**Pathologic significance**

- It eliminates cells that are injured beyond repair without eliciting a host reaction, which limits collateral tissue damage.
- When DNA damage cannot be corrected by repair mechanisms, the cell triggers apoptosis. This has a protective effect, because it prevents the survival of cells with DNA mutations that can lead to malignant transformation.
- p53 triggers apoptosis by the mitochondrial pathway if DNA repair fails. Mutations in p53 that interfere with this are associated with numerous cancers.
- Cytotoxic T lymphocytes induce apoptosis of virus-infected cells, in an attempt to eliminate reservoirs of infection, and kill tumour cells.
- The same CTL-mediated mechanism causes cellular rejection of transplants and tissue damage in graft-versus-host disease.

## Give examples of apoptosis in physiological conditions.

1. **Removal** of supernumerary cells during development, with involution of primordial structures and remodelling of maturing tissues.
2. **Involution** of hormone-dependent tissues on hormone withdrawal: endometrial cell breakdown during the menstrual cycle, ovarian follicular atresia in menopause and regression of the lactating breast after weaning.
3. **Cell turnover** in proliferating cell populations, such as epithelial cells in intestinal crypts.
4. **Elimination of lymphocytes** that fail to produce functional antigen receptors: immature lymphocytes in the bone marrow and thymus and germinal centre B cells.
5. **Elimination** of potentially harmful self-reactive lymphocytes.
6. **Death of host cells** that have served their useful purpose: neutrophils in an acute inflammatory response and lymphocytes at the end of an immune response.

## Give two examples of apoptosis in pathological conditions.

1. **DNA damage:** radiation and cytotoxic anticancer drugs damage DNA, either directly or through production of free radicals. If repair mechanisms cannot correct the damage, the cell triggers apoptosis, which has a protective effect by preventing the survival of cells with DNA mutations that can lead to malignant transformation.
2. **Viral infections:** apoptosis is induced by the virus itself, as in adenovirus and HIV infections, or by the host immune response, as in viral hepatitis. Cytotoxic T lymphocytes specific for viral proteins induce apoptosis of infected cells to eliminate reservoirs of infection.

## Write short note on: Apoptosis

**Definition:** **Apoptosis** is a type of cell death that is induced by a tightly regulated suicide programme in which cells destined to die activate intrinsic enzymes that degrade cellular DNA and nuclear and cytoplasmic proteins.

- Apoptotic cells break up into plasma membrane-bound fragments, called apoptotic bodies, which contain portions of the cytoplasm and nucleus.
- The dead cell and its fragments are rapidly devoured before the contents leak out, so apoptosis does not elicit an inflammatory reaction.

**Causes**

Physiologic situations

- Removal of supernumerary cells during development (programmed cell death).
- Involution of hormone-dependent tissues on hormone withdrawal, such as endometrial cell breakdown during the menstrual cycle, ovarian follicular atresia in menopause and regression of the lactating breast after weaning.
- Cell turnover in proliferating cell populations, such as epithelial cells in intestinal crypts.
- Elimination of lymphocytes that fail to produce functional antigen receptors.
- Elimination of potentially harmful self-reactive lymphocytes.
- Death of host cells that have served their useful purpose, such as neutrophils in an acute inflammatory response and lymphocytes at the end of an immune response.

Pathologic conditions

- DNA damage, from radiation and cytotoxic anticancer drugs.
- Accumulation of misfolded proteins, with ER stress.
- Infections, particularly viral infections.
- Killing by cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) of virus-infected cells and tumour cells, cellular rejection of transplants and graft-versus-host disease.
- Pathologic atrophy of parenchymal organs after duct obstruction, as in the pancreas, parotid gland and kidney.

**Pathogenesis**

- Apoptosis results from activation of **caspases**, proteases that have a cysteine in their active site and cleave proteins after aspartic residues.
- Caspases exist as inactive proenzymes and must be cleaved to become active.
- In the initiation phase some caspases become active and unleash a cascade of other caspases. In the execution phase the terminal caspases trigger cellular fragmentation and demise.
- Two pathways converge on caspase activation: the mitochondrial pathway and the death receptor pathway.

**Mitochondrial (intrinsic) pathway**

- It is responsible for apoptosis in most physiologic and pathologic situations.
- Loss of survival signals, DNA damage and ER stress upregulate the BH3-only proteins, which activate **BAX** and **BAK** and block the anti-apoptotic proteins BCL2 and BCL-XL.
- Increased permeability of the outer mitochondrial membrane releases **cytochrome c** into the cytoplasm.
- Cytochrome c binds **APAF-1** to form the **apoptosome**, which activates **caspase-9**.
- Active caspase-9 activates other pro-caspases, such as caspase-3.

**Death receptor (extrinsic) pathway**

- Death receptors belong to the TNF receptor family and have a cytoplasmic death domain. The best known are **TNFR1** and **Fas (CD95)**.
- Fas ligand binds Fas, and the adaptor protein **FADD** binds inactive **caspase-8** (or caspase-10), which is cleaved to its active form.
- Active caspase-8 initiates the same executioner caspase sequence as the mitochondrial pathway.

**Execution phase and removal**

- Executioner caspases, caspase-3 and caspase-6, cleave an inhibitor of a DNase, so DNA degradation begins, and they proteolyse the nuclear matrix, which causes nuclear fragmentation.
- Phosphatidylserine flips to the outer layer of the plasma membrane, and apoptotic bodies are coated with natural antibodies and complement proteins, notably C1q. These are the "eat me" signals for phagocytes.

**Morphology**

1. **Cell shrinkage:** the cell is reduced in size, the cytoplasm is dense and eosinophilic, and the organelles are more tightly packed.
2. **Chromatin condensation:** the most characteristic feature. The chromatin aggregates peripherally under the nuclear membrane into dense masses, and the nucleus may break up into two or more fragments.
3. **Formation of cytoplasmic blebs and apoptotic bodies:** extensive surface membrane blebbing is followed by fragmentation into membrane-bound apoptotic bodies.
4. **Phagocytosis** of apoptotic cells or cell bodies, usually by macrophages. On H&E the apoptotic cell is a round or oval mass of intensely eosinophilic cytoplasm with fragments of dense nuclear chromatin.

## What are the causes of apoptosis?

**Physiologic situations**

- Removal of supernumerary cells (in excess of the required number) during development. Cell death is critical for involution of primordial structures and remodelling of maturing tissues.
- Involution of hormone-dependent tissues on hormone withdrawal, such as endometrial cell breakdown during the menstrual cycle, ovarian follicular atresia in menopause and regression of the lactating breast after weaning.
- Cell turnover in proliferating cell populations, such as epithelial cells in intestinal crypts, to maintain constant cell numbers (homeostasis).
- Elimination of lymphocytes that fail to produce functional antigen receptors, such as immature lymphocytes in the bone marrow and thymus and germinal centre B cells.
- Elimination of potentially harmful self-reactive lymphocytes, to prevent immune reactions against one's own tissues.
- Death of host cells that have served their useful purpose, such as neutrophils in an acute inflammatory response and lymphocytes at the end of an immune response.

In all of these situations, cells undergo apoptosis because they are deprived of necessary survival signals, such as growth factors and interactions with the extracellular matrix, or they receive pro-apoptotic signals from other cells or the surrounding environment.

**Pathologic conditions**

- **DNA damage:** radiation and cytotoxic anticancer drugs can damage DNA, either directly or through production of free radicals.
- **Accumulation of misfolded proteins:** cell death triggered by improperly folded intracellular proteins and the subsequent ER stress response.
- **Infections**, particularly viral infections, because of the virus itself (as in adenovirus and HIV infections) or the host immune response (as in viral hepatitis).
- Killing by **cytotoxic T lymphocytes**, which is responsible for killing tumour cells, cellular rejection of transplants and tissue damage in graft-versus-host disease.
- Pathologic atrophy in parenchymal organs after duct obstruction, such as occurs in the pancreas, parotid gland and kidney.

## Write down the differences between necrosis and apoptosis.

| Feature | Necrosis | Apoptosis |
|---|---|---|
| **Cell size** | Enlarged (swelling) | Reduced (shrinkage) |
| **Nucleus** | Pyknosis, karyorrhexis, karyolysis | Fragmentation into nucleosome-size fragments |
| **Plasma membrane** | Disrupted | Intact; altered structure, especially orientation of lipids |
| **Cellular contents** | Enzymatic digestion; may leak out of the cell | Intact; may be released in apoptotic bodies |
| **Adjacent inflammation** | Frequent | No |
| **Physiologic or pathologic role** | Usually pathologic (culmination of irreversible cell injury) | Often physiologic, a means of eliminating unwanted cells; may be pathologic after some forms of cell injury, especially DNA damage |
| **Regulation** | Historically thought of as accidental cell death | Regulated cell death, mediated by defined molecular pathways |
| **Cytoplasm** | Eosinophilic, glassy and homogeneous; vacuolated and moth-eaten once organelles are digested | Dense and eosinophilic |
## Examples of pathological apoptosis.

1. **DNA damage:** radiation and cytotoxic anticancer drugs damage DNA, either directly or through production of free radicals.
2. **Accumulation of misfolded proteins:** cell death is triggered by improperly folded intracellular proteins and the subsequent ER stress response.
3. **Infections**, particularly viral infections: apoptosis is induced by the virus itself, as in adenovirus and HIV infections, or by the host immune response, as in viral hepatitis.
4. **Killing by cytotoxic T lymphocytes:** the mechanism responsible for killing tumour cells, cellular rejection of transplants and tissue damage in graft-versus-host disease.
