5. **Pathologic atrophy** in parenchymal organs after duct obstruction, as in the pancreas, parotid gland and kidney.

## Morphological changes in an apoptotic cell.

1. **Cell shrinkage:** cell size is reduced, the cytoplasm is dense and eosinophilic, and the organelles, although relatively normal, are more tightly packed. This contrasts with necrosis, in which an early feature is cell swelling.
2. **Chromatin condensation:** the most characteristic feature. The chromatin aggregates peripherally, under the nuclear membrane, into dense masses of various shapes and sizes. The nucleus itself may break up into two or more fragments.
3. **Formation of cytoplasmic blebs and apoptotic bodies:** the cell first shows extensive surface membrane blebbing, followed by fragmentation into membrane-bound apoptotic bodies composed of cytoplasm and tightly packed organelles, with or without nuclear fragments.
4. **Phagocytosis** of apoptotic cells or cell bodies, usually by macrophages. The apoptotic bodies are rapidly ingested by phagocytes and degraded by their lysosomal enzymes.

- On H&E the apoptotic cell appears as a round or oval mass of intensely eosinophilic cytoplasm with fragments of dense nuclear chromatin.
- Because the cell shrinkage and formation of apoptotic bodies are rapid and the pieces are quickly cleared, considerable apoptosis may occur in tissues before it is apparent in histologic sections.
- The absence of an inflammatory response can also make apoptosis difficult to detect by light microscopy.

## Nuclear changes in apoptosis.

- **Chromatin condensation** is the most characteristic feature of apoptosis. The chromatin aggregates peripherally, under the nuclear membrane, into dense masses of various shapes and sizes.
- On electron microscopy some nuclei show peripheral crescents of compacted chromatin, and others are uniformly dense or fragmented.
- The nucleus itself may break up into two or more fragments.
- **Pyknosis**, with nuclear shrinkage and increased basophilia, is also seen in apoptotic cell death.
- The nucleus is fragmented into nucleosome-size fragments.
- Executioner caspases cleave an inhibitor of a DNase, so the DNase becomes enzymatically active and DNA degradation commences.
- Caspases also proteolyse structural components of the nuclear matrix, which promotes fragmentation of nuclei.

## Give the pathogenesis of apoptosis.

**General mechanism**

- Apoptosis results from activation of **caspases**, proteases that have a cysteine in their active site and cleave proteins after aspartic residues.
- Caspases exist as inactive proenzymes and must be cleaved to become active.
- Active caspases are therefore a marker of apoptosis.
- **Initiation phase:** some caspases become catalytically active and unleash a cascade of other caspases.
- **Execution phase:** the terminal caspases trigger cellular fragmentation and demise.
- Regulation depends on a balance between pro-apoptotic and anti-apoptotic proteins.
- Two pathways converge on caspase activation: the mitochondrial pathway and the death receptor pathway.

**Mitochondrial (intrinsic) pathway**

- It is responsible for apoptosis in most physiologic and pathologic situations.

1. Increased permeability of the outer mitochondrial membrane releases pro-apoptotic molecules, such as **cytochrome c**, into the cytoplasm.

2. The **BCL2 family** controls the integrity of the outer membrane. It has three groups.

- Anti-apoptotic: BCL2, BCL-XL and MCL1, which keep the membrane impermeable and prevent leakage of cytochrome c.
- Pro-apoptotic: **BAX** and **BAK**, which on activation oligomerise in the outer membrane and enhance its permeability.
- Regulated initiators: the BH3-only proteins BAD, BIM, BID, Puma and Noxa, which respond to cellular stress and damage.

3. Loss of survival signals such as growth factors, DNA damage, or ER stress from misfolded proteins upregulates the BH3-only proteins.

4. BH3-only proteins activate BAX and BAK, and also bind and block BCL2 and BCL-XL.

5. Synthesis of BCL2 and BCL-XL declines, because it depends on survival signals.

6. Cytochrome c binds **APAF-1** to form the **apoptosome**.

7. The apoptosome binds **caspase-9**, the critical initiator caspase of this pathway, and promotes its autocatalytic cleavage.

8. Active caspase-9 activates other pro-caspases, such as caspase-3.

9. **Smac/DIABLO** enter the cytoplasm and neutralise the inhibitors of apoptosis (**IAPs**), which permits the caspase cascade.

**Death receptor (extrinsic) pathway**

1. Death receptors belong to the **TNF receptor family** and have a cytoplasmic **death domain**. The best known are **TNFR1** and **Fas (CD95)**.

2. Fas ligand (FasL) is expressed on T cells that recognise self-antigens, which eliminates self-reactive lymphocytes, and on some cytotoxic T lymphocytes that kill virus-infected and tumour cells.

3. FasL binds Fas and brings three or more Fas molecules together. Their death domains form a binding site for the adaptor protein **FADD**.

4. FADD binds inactive **caspase-8** (or caspase-10), which undergoes autocatalytic cleavage to the active form.

5. Active caspase-8 initiates the same executioner caspase sequence as the mitochondrial pathway.

6. **FLIP** inhibits the pathway by binding pro-caspase-8 and blocking FADD binding. Some viruses and normal cells produce FLIP.

7. In hepatocytes and pancreatic beta cells, caspase-8 cleaves and activates the BH3-only protein **BID**, which feeds into the mitochondrial pathway.

**Execution phase**

- Caspase-9 (mitochondrial pathway) and caspase-8 and caspase-10 (death receptor pathway) activate the executioner caspases, caspase-3 and caspase-6.
- Executioner caspases cleave an inhibitor of a DNase, so the DNase becomes active and DNA degradation begins.
- They also proteolyse the nuclear matrix, which causes nuclear fragmentation.

**Removal of dead cells**

- Apoptotic bodies are bite-sized fragments that phagocytes can engulf.
- **Phosphatidylserine** flips from the inner to the outer leaflet of the plasma membrane, where macrophage receptors recognise it.
- Dying cells secrete soluble factors that recruit phagocytes.
- Apoptotic bodies are coated with natural antibodies and complement proteins, notably **C1q**.
- These are the "eat me" signals, and the phagocytosis is called **efferocytosis**.
- Macrophages that ingest apoptotic cells make fewer proinflammatory cytokines, which limits inflammation.
## Why does inflammation usually not occur in apoptosis?

- The plasma membrane remains intact, but its surface components are altered to produce "find me" and "eat me" signals for phagocytes.
- Apoptotic bodies break the cell up into "bite-sized" fragments that are edible for phagocytes.
- The dead cell and its fragments are rapidly devoured before the contents leak out.
- **Phosphatidylserine** flips from the inner leaflet of the plasma membrane to the outer layer, where it is recognised by several macrophage receptors.
- Dying cells secrete soluble factors that recruit phagocytes, and macrophages produce proteins that bind to apoptotic cells but not to live cells.
- Apoptotic bodies may be coated with natural antibodies and proteins of the complement system, notably **C1q**.
- This phagocytosis is called **efferocytosis**. It is so efficient that dead cells disappear, often within minutes, without leaving a trace.
- Production of proinflammatory cytokines is reduced in macrophages that have ingested apoptotic cells, which limits inflammatory reactions even in the face of extensive apoptosis.

## What do you mean by intracellular accumulation?

One of the manifestations of metabolic derangements in cells is the **intracellular accumulation** of substances that may be harmless or cause further injury. These accumulations may be located in the cytoplasm, within organelles (typically lysosomes), or in the nucleus, and they may be composed of substances that are synthesised by the affected cells or are produced elsewhere.

## Examples of intracellular accumulation.

| Substance | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Lipids** | Triglycerides in fatty change (steatosis) of the liver, heart, muscle and kidney; cholesterol and cholesterol esters in foam cells of atherosclerosis, xanthomas, cholesterolosis of the gallbladder and Niemann-Pick disease [sphingomyelinase deficiency, a lysosomal storage disease] type C (a lysosomal storage disease with cholesterol accumulation); phospholipids in the myelin figures of necrotic cells |
| **Proteins** | Reabsorption droplets in proximal renal tubules in proteinuria; Russell bodies [large homogeneous eosinophilic inclusions from a distended ER] in plasma cells making immunoglobulins; alpha-1 antitrypsin in hepatocytes; alcoholic hyaline (keratin filaments) in liver cells; neurofibrillary tangle in Alzheimer disease [a degenerative disease] |
| **Glycogen** | Diabetes mellitus, in renal tubular epithelial cells, liver cells, beta cells of the islets of Langerhans and heart muscle cells; glycogen storage diseases |
| **Pigments** | Exogenous: carbon (anthracosis) and tattoo pigments. Endogenous: lipofuscin, melanin, haemosiderin and homogentisic acid (alkaptonuria, with ochronosis) |

## Define fatty change.

The terms **steatosis** and **fatty change** describe abnormal accumulations of triglycerides within parenchymal cells.

## Causes of fatty change of the liver.

- The causes of steatosis include **toxins**, protein malnutrition, diabetes mellitus, obesity and anoxia.
- In higher-income nations the most common causes of significant fatty change in the liver (fatty liver) are **excessive alcohol use** and nonalcoholic fatty liver disease, which is often associated with diabetes and obesity.

## Alcohol abuse with massive hepatomegaly; liver biopsy shows fat droplets in hepatocytes — Diagnosis? Future consequence?

**Diagnosis:** alcohol-associated **fatty liver** (hepatic steatosis, or fatty change).

- Hepatic steatosis is an early, predictable effect of alcohol consumption.
- Lipid droplets accumulate in hepatocytes, coalesce into large droplets, distend the hepatocyte and push the nucleus aside.
- Macroscopically the fatty liver of chronic alcohol use disorder is enlarged, soft, yellow and greasy.

**Future consequences**

1. Fatty change is completely reversible if there is abstention from further intake of alcohol.
2. With continued injury, **alcohol-associated hepatitis** (steatohepatitis) may develop, with ballooned hepatocytes, Mallory hyaline (skeins of keratin intermediate filaments), neutrophils and spotty necrosis or apoptosis.
3. Fibrosis follows, starting as pericellular fibrosis with a "chicken wire" appearance. It progresses to portal and periportal fibrosis, bridging fibrosis and **cirrhosis**, which is often micronodular (Laennec cirrhosis [micronodular cirrhosis, classically alcoholic], the micronodular cirrhosis of alcohol-associated liver disease).
4. Early scarring can regress with cessation of alcohol use, but complete regression of alcohol-associated cirrhosis is rare.
5. Only 10% to 15% of patients with alcohol use disorder develop cirrhosis.
6. Hepatocellular carcinoma occurs mostly in chronic liver disease with cirrhosis, and alcoholic liver disease is one of the most common underlying diseases.

## Define hyaline change.

The term **hyaline** usually refers to an alteration within cells or in the extracellular space that gives a homogeneous, glassy, pink appearance in routine histologic sections stained with H&E.

## Examples of hyaline change.

**Intracellular hyaline accumulations of protein**

- Reabsorption droplets in proximal renal tubules.
- Russell bodies [large homogeneous eosinophilic inclusions in plasma cells].
- Alcoholic hyaline (eosinophilic inclusion in liver cells, composed predominantly of keratin intermediate filaments).

**Extracellular hyaline**

- Collagenous fibres in old scars may appear hyalinised.
- In long-standing hypertension and diabetes mellitus, the walls of arterioles, especially in the kidney, become hyalinised, from extravasated plasma protein and deposition of basement membrane material.

## Define pathological calcification.

**Pathologic calcification** is the abnormal tissue deposition of calcium salts, together with smaller amounts of iron, magnesium, and other mineral salts.

## Write short note on: Pathological calcification

**Definition:** **Pathologic calcification** is the abnormal tissue deposition of calcium salts, together with smaller amounts of iron, magnesium, and other mineral salts.

**Types and causes**

- **Dystrophic calcification:** deposition occurs locally in dying tissues. It occurs despite normal serum levels of calcium and in the absence of derangements in calcium metabolism.
- **Metastatic calcification:** deposition of calcium salts in otherwise normal tissues. It almost always results from **hypercalcaemia** secondary to some disturbance in calcium metabolism.

Four principal causes of hypercalcaemia

1. Increased secretion of parathyroid hormone with bone resorption, as in hyperparathyroidism due to parathyroid tumours and ectopic secretion of parathyroid hormone-related protein by malignant tumours.
2. Resorption of bone tissue, secondary to primary tumours of bone marrow (for example multiple myeloma), diffuse skeletal metastasis (for example breast cancer), accelerated bone turnover (for example Paget disease [disordered bone remodelling], a disorder of accelerated bone turnover) or immobilisation.
3. Vitamin D-related disorders: vitamin D intoxication, sarcoidosis (in which macrophages activate a vitamin D precursor) and idiopathic hypercalcaemia of infancy (Williams syndrome [elastin gene deletion with supravalvular aortic stenosis], with abnormal sensitivity to vitamin D).
4. Renal failure, which causes retention of phosphate and leads to secondary hyperparathyroidism.

**Sites**

- Dystrophic: areas of coagulative, caseous or liquefactive necrosis, foci of enzymatic necrosis of fat, the atheromas of advanced atherosclerosis, and aging or damaged heart valves.
- Metastatic: interstitial tissues of the gastric mucosa, kidneys, lungs, systemic arteries and pulmonary veins. All of these tissues excrete acid and have an internal alkaline compartment.

**Morphology**

- Macroscopically the calcium salts appear as fine, white granules or clumps, often felt as gritty deposits. A tuberculous lymph node may be virtually converted to stone.
- On H&E the calcium salts have a basophilic, amorphous granular, sometimes clumped appearance. They can be intracellular, extracellular or in both locations.
- Heterotopic bone may form in the focus of calcification.
- In metastatic calcification the salts resemble those of dystrophic calcification, as noncrystalline amorphous deposits or hydroxyapatite crystals.

**Clinical significance**

- Dystrophic calcification may simply be a telltale sign of previous cell injury, but it is often a cause of organ dysfunction, as in calcific valvular disease and atherosclerosis.
- Metastatic calcification usually causes no clinical dysfunction. Massive involvement of the lungs produces respiratory compromise, and massive deposits in the kidney (nephrocalcinosis) may in time cause renal damage.

## Write down the difference between dystrophic and metastatic calcification.

| Feature | Dystrophic calcification | Metastatic calcification |
|---|---|---|
| **Tissue involved** | Dying tissues | Otherwise normal tissues |
| **Serum calcium** | Normal; no derangement of calcium metabolism | Hypercalcaemia, secondary to some disturbance in calcium metabolism |
| **Cause** | Necrotic cells and cellular debris that are not promptly destroyed and reabsorbed provide a nidus for deposition of calcium salts | Increased parathyroid hormone secretion; resorption of bone tissue; vitamin D-related disorders; renal failure with retention of phosphate |
| **Sites** | Areas of coagulative, caseous or liquefactive necrosis; foci of enzymatic necrosis of fat; atheromas of advanced atherosclerosis; aging or damaged heart valves; tuberculous lymph node | Interstitial tissues of the gastric mucosa, kidneys, lungs, systemic arteries and pulmonary veins, which all excrete acid and have an internal alkaline compartment |
| **Morphology** | Fine white granules or clumps, gritty; basophilic, amorphous and granular on H&E | Resembles dystrophic calcification; noncrystalline amorphous deposits or hydroxyapatite crystals |
| **Clinical effect** | Often a cause of organ dysfunction, as in calcific valvular disease and atherosclerosis | Usually no clinical dysfunction; massive lung involvement causes respiratory compromise and massive kidney deposits (nephrocalcinosis) may cause renal damage |

## Sites of dystrophic calcification.

- **Areas of necrosis**, whether of coagulative, caseous or liquefactive type.
- **Foci** of enzymatic necrosis of fat.
- **Atheromas of advanced atherosclerosis**, where calcification is almost always present.
- **Aging or damaged** heart valves, as in calcific aortic stenosis.
- **A tuberculous lymph node**, which may be virtually converted to stone.

## Common sites of metastatic calcification.

- **Interstitial tissues** of the gastric mucosa, kidneys, lungs, systemic arteries and pulmonary veins.
- All of these tissues excrete **acid** and therefore have an internal **alkaline compartment** that predisposes them to metastatic calcification.

## Define dystrophic calcification.

**Dystrophic calcification** is the deposition of calcium salts locally in dying tissues. It occurs despite normal serum levels of calcium and in the absence of derangements in calcium metabolism.

## Write short note on: Dystrophic calcification

**Definition:** **Dystrophic calcification** is the deposition of calcium salts locally in dying tissues. It occurs despite normal serum levels of calcium and in the absence of derangements in calcium metabolism.

**Pathogenesis**

- If necrotic cells and cellular debris are not promptly destroyed and reabsorbed, they provide a nidus for the deposition of calcium salts and other minerals, and tend to become calcified.
- Single necrotic cells may constitute seed crystals that become encrusted by the mineral deposits.

**Sites**

- Areas of necrosis, whether of coagulative, caseous or liquefactive type.
- Foci of enzymatic necrosis of fat.
- Atheromas of advanced atherosclerosis, where calcification is almost always present.
- Aging or damaged heart valves, as in calcific aortic stenosis.
- Tuberculous lymph node, which may be virtually converted to stone.

**Morphology**

- Macroscopically: fine, white granules or clumps, often felt as gritty deposits.
- On H&E: basophilic, amorphous granular, sometimes clumped calcium salts, intracellular, extracellular or in both locations.
- Heterotopic bone may form in the focus of calcification.
- Progressive acquisition of outer layers creates lamellated configurations called **psammoma bodies**, which are seen in some papillary cancers, for example of the thyroid.
- In asbestosis, calcium and iron salts gather about long slender spicules of asbestos in the lung, creating beaded dumbbell forms known as **asbestos bodies**.

**Clinical significance**

- It may simply be a telltale sign of previous cell injury.
- It is often a cause of organ dysfunction, as in calcific valvular disease and atherosclerosis.
- Serum calcium is normal.

## Define inflammation.

**Inflammation** is a response of vascularised tissues that delivers leucocytes and molecules of host defence from the circulation to sites of infection and cell damage.

## Types of inflammation.

| Type | Features |
|---|---|
| **Acute inflammation** | Rapid, often self-limited response to offending agents that are readily eliminated, such as many bacteria and fungi, and dead cells; develops within minutes or hours and lasts several hours to a few days; characterised by exudation of fluid and plasma proteins (oedema) and emigration of leucocytes, predominantly neutrophils |
| **Chronic inflammation** | May follow acute inflammation or arise de novo; response to agents that are difficult to eradicate; longer duration, with more tissue destruction and scarring (fibrosis) |
| **Local inflammation** | Largely confined to the site of infection or damage |
| **Systemic inflammation** | Rare; seen in some disseminated bacterial infections, where it is called sepsis; systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS) is a sepsis-like syndrome not caused by microbial infections |

## Examples of each type of inflammation.

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Acute** | Acute respiratory distress syndrome (neutrophils); glomerulonephritis and vasculitis (antibodies, complement, neutrophils, monocytes); septic shock (cytokines) |
| **Chronic** | Infection with tubercle bacilli; rheumatoid arthritis (lymphocytes, macrophages); asthma (eosinophils, IgE antibodies), which can present as acute inflammation or as a chronic illness with repeated acute exacerbations; atherosclerosis; lung fibrosis; peptic ulcers, in which chronic inflammation coexists with unresolved acute inflammation |
| **Local** | Bacterial or viral pharyngitis, where fever is a systemic manifestation of a local reaction |
| **Systemic** | Sepsis; SIRS caused by burns, trauma and pancreatitis |

## Causes of inflammation.

1. **Infections** (bacterial, viral, fungal, parasitic) and microbial toxins: among the most common and medically important causes. Different pathogens elicit responses ranging from mild acute inflammation to severe systemic reactions that can be fatal, to prolonged chronic reactions that cause extensive tissue injury.
2. **Tissue necrosis:** it elicits inflammation regardless of the cause of cell death, whether ischaemia (as in myocardial infarction), trauma, or physical and chemical injury such as burns, frostbite, irradiation and exposure to some environmental chemicals. Molecules released from necrotic cells trigger this sterile inflammation.
3. **Foreign bodies**, such as splinters, dirt and sutures, which may elicit inflammation by themselves or because they cause traumatic tissue injury or carry microbes. Endogenous substances that deposit in tissues can also be harmful, such as urate crystals in gout and cholesterol crystals in atherosclerosis.
4. **Immune reactions (hypersensitivity):** the immune system damages the individual's own tissues. The reactions may be directed against self antigens, causing autoimmune diseases, or against environmental substances, as in allergies, or against microbes. These reactions tend to be persistent and are often associated with chronic inflammation.

## What are the cardinal signs of acute inflammation?

| Sign | Meaning | Cause |
|---|---|---|
| **Rubor** | Redness | Increased blood flow, with engorgement of small vessels by slowly moving red cells |
| **Calor** | Heat | Increased blood flow |
| **Tumor** | Swelling | Increased blood flow and oedema, the accumulation of extravascular fluid (exudate) rich in plasma proteins |
| **Dolor** | Pain | Tissue damage and the liberation of prostaglandins, neuropeptides and cytokines |
| **Functio laesa** | Loss of function | Pain and injury to the tissues |

- The first four signs were listed by **Celsus** (a Roman writer of the first century CE).
- The fifth sign, loss of function, was added by **Virchow** (the father of modern pathology).

## How/why does rubor (redness) occur?

- **Vasodilation** is induced by several mediators, notably **histamine**, acting on vascular smooth muscle.
- It involves mainly the postcapillary venules and results in increased blood flow, which causes redness (erythema) and heat at the site of inflammation.
- Loss of fluid and increased vessel diameter slow the blood flow (**stasis**) and increase the concentration of red cells in small vessels.
- The small vessels become engorged with slowly moving red cells, which causes redness of the inflamed area. This is seen histologically as **vascular congestion**.

## How does calor (heat) occur?

- Heat (calor) results from **increased blood flow** to the injured area.
- Vasodilation, induced by mediators such as **histamine**, involves mainly the postcapillary venules.
- The increased blood flow is the cause of heat and redness (erythema) at the site of inflammation.

## Why does swelling develop in inflammation?

- Swelling (tumor) is caused by **increased blood flow and oedema**.
- Vasodilation is quickly followed by increased permeability of the microvasculature, with outpouring of protein-rich fluid into the extravascular tissues.
- The escape of fluid, proteins and blood cells from the vascular system into the interstitial tissue is called **exudation**.
- The extravascular fluid, rich in plasma proteins, is known as oedema.
- Lymph flow is increased and helps to drain the oedema fluid that accumulates.

## What are the components of acute inflammation?

1. **Dilation of small vessels**, leading to an increase in blood flow.
2. **Increased permeability of the microvasculature**, enabling plasma proteins and leucocytes to leave the circulation.
3. **Emigration of leucocytes** from the microcirculation, their accumulation in the focus of injury, and their activation to eliminate the offending agent.

- These form the two main components of inflammation: a vascular reaction and a cellular response, both activated by mediators derived from plasma proteins and various cells.

## Hallmark of acute inflammation.

- Increased permeability of postcapillary venules is a **hallmark of acute inflammation**.
- The morphologic hallmarks are dilation of small blood vessels and accumulation of leucocytes and fluid in the extravascular tissue.
- Acute inflammation is characterised by exudation of fluid and plasma proteins (oedema) and emigration of leucocytes, predominantly neutrophils.

## Name the acute inflammatory cells.

- **Neutrophils** and **macrophages** are the most important leucocytes, because they are capable of phagocytosis.
- Neutrophils predominate in the infiltrate during the first 6 to 24 hours.
- They are replaced by monocytes, which become macrophages, in 24 to 48 hours.
- Exceptions: in Pseudomonas infections neutrophils dominate for several days, in viral infections lymphocytes may arrive first, and in helminthic infections and allergic reactions eosinophils may be the main cell type.
- Some T lymphocytes, the Th17 cells that produce IL-17, also contribute to acute inflammation.

## Name the chronic inflammatory cells.

- **Macrophages** are the dominant cells in most chronic inflammatory reactions.
- **Lymphocytes**, both T and B cells.
- **Plasma cells**, the antibody-secreting cells derived from activated B lymphocytes.
- **Eosinophils**, abundant in reactions mediated by Th2 cells, such as parasitic infections and some allergies.
- **Mast cells**, widely distributed in connective tissues.
- **Neutrophils**, which persist in many forms of chronic inflammation, for example in chronic bacterial infection of bone (osteomyelitis).
- Activated macrophages may become **epithelioid cells** or fuse to form **giant cells**.

## What are the differences between acute and chronic inflammation?

| Feature | Acute inflammation | Chronic inflammation |
|---|---|---|
| **Onset** | Rapid; develops within minutes or hours | May follow acute inflammation or arise de novo |
| **Duration** | Short, from several hours to a few days | Longer, weeks or months |
| **Agent** | Offending agents that are readily eliminated, such as many bacteria and fungi, and dead cells | Agents that are difficult to eradicate: some bacteria (tubercle bacilli), viruses, fungi, self antigens, environmental antigens and toxic substances such as silica |
| **Principal cells** | Neutrophils | Mononuclear cells: macrophages, lymphocytes and plasma cells |
| **Main features** | Vascular changes and exudation of fluid and plasma proteins (oedema) | Infiltration with mononuclear cells, tissue destruction, and attempts at healing by angiogenesis and fibrosis |
| **Tissue injury** | Often self-limited; if the stimulus is eliminated the reaction subsides and residual injury is repaired | More tissue destruction and scarring (fibrosis) |
| **Outcome** | Resolution, healing by scarring, or progression to chronic inflammation | Coexisting inflammation, tissue injury and attempted repair by scarring |

## Explain: "Inflammation is a protective response but may be potentially harmful."

**Inflammation** is a response of vascularised tissues that delivers leucocytes and molecules of host defence from the circulation to sites of infection and cell damage.

**It is protective**

- It is a response that is essential for survival.
- It rids the host of both the initial cause of cell injury (for example microbes and toxins) and the consequences of such injury (necrotic cells and tissues).
- Phagocytic leucocytes, antibodies and complement proteins are recruited to the site. The stimulus is removed mainly by phagocytes, and repair follows by regeneration and scarring.

**It may be harmful**

- Protective reactions to infection are often accompanied by local tissue damage, with pain and functional impairment. These effects are usually self-limited and leave little or no permanent damage.
- In many diseases the reaction is misdirected against self tissues (autoimmune diseases), occurs against normally harmless environmental substances (allergies) or is inadequately controlled. The protective reaction then becomes the cause of the disease.
- Activated leucocytes release enzymes and reactive oxygen species into the extracellular space, and these damage normal tissue.
- Examples of diseases caused by inflammatory injury are acute respiratory distress syndrome (neutrophils), glomerulonephritis and vasculitis (antibodies, complement, neutrophils and monocytes), septic shock (cytokines), rheumatoid arthritis (lymphocytes and macrophages) and asthma (eosinophils and IgE antibodies).
- Inflammation may also contribute to type 2 diabetes, Alzheimer disease [progressive dementia with amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles] and cancer.

## Vascular changes / reactions of blood vessels in acute inflammation.

1. **Vasodilation:** one of the earliest manifestations, involving mainly postcapillary venules and causing increased blood flow.
2. **Increased permeability of the microvasculature:** outpouring of protein-rich fluid into the extravascular tissues.
3. **Stasis:** loss of fluid and increased vessel diameter slow the blood flow and increase the concentration of red cells, with vascular congestion.
4. **Leucocyte accumulation:** as stasis develops, leucocytes, principally neutrophils, accumulate along the endothelium. The endothelial cells are activated and express increased levels of adhesion molecules. Leucocytes then adhere and migrate into the interstitial tissue.

- The vascular reactions are designed to maximise the movement of plasma proteins and leucocytes out of the circulation and into the site of infection or injury.
- Lymphatic vessels also take part. Lymph flow is increased and helps to drain oedema fluid, and the lymphatics and draining lymph nodes may become inflamed (lymphangitis and lymphadenitis).

## How does increased vascular permeability / vascular leakage occur in acute inflammation?

Increased permeability of the postcapillary venules, a hallmark of acute inflammation, has two main mechanisms.

1. **Contraction of endothelial cells**, which opens interendothelial gaps. It is the most common mechanism of vascular leakage and is elicited by histamine, bradykinin, leukotrienes and other chemical mediators.

- Immediate transient response: it occurs rapidly after exposure to the mediators and is usually short lived, lasting 15 to 30 minutes.
- Delayed prolonged leakage: in some forms of mild injury (burns, irradiation or ultraviolet radiation, certain bacterial toxins) leakage begins after a delay of 2 to 12 hours and lasts for several hours or even days. It may be caused by contraction of endothelial cells or mild endothelial damage. Sunburn is the classic example.

2. **Endothelial injury**, resulting in endothelial cell necrosis and detachment. It is seen in severe physical injuries such as thermal burns, or is induced by microbes and microbial toxins. Leakage starts immediately and is sustained for several hours until the damaged vessels are thrombosed or repaired.

- Neutrophils that adhere to the endothelium may also injure endothelial cells and amplify the reaction.
- All these mechanisms probably contribute in varying degrees to most responses. At different stages of a thermal burn, for example, leakage results from chemically mediated endothelial contraction and from direct and leucocyte-dependent endothelial injury.

## How does edema (exudative edema) occur in acute inflammation?

- **Oedema** denotes an excess of fluid in the interstitial tissue or serous cavities. It can be either an exudate or a transudate.
- Vasodilation is quickly followed by increased permeability of the microvasculature, by contraction of endothelial cells (histamine, bradykinin, leukotrienes) and by endothelial injury.
- Protein-rich fluid escapes from the vessels into the extravascular tissue. This escape of fluid, proteins and blood cells is called **exudation**.
- An **exudate** is an extravascular fluid with a high protein concentration that may contain cellular debris or, occasionally, some blood cells. Its presence implies an inflammatory process that has increased the permeability of small blood vessels.
- In contrast, a **transudate** has a low protein content, mostly albumin, and little or no cellular material. It is an ultrafiltrate of plasma produced by osmotic or hydrostatic imbalance across the vessel wall, without an increase in vascular permeability.
- Lymph flow is increased and helps to drain the oedema fluid.

## What are the vascular events in acute inflammation?

1. **Vasodilation:** one of the earliest manifestations, involving mainly postcapillary venules and causing increased blood flow.
2. **Increased permeability of the microvasculature:** outpouring of protein-rich fluid into the extravascular tissues.
3. **Stasis:** loss of fluid and increased vessel diameter slow the blood flow and increase the concentration of red cells, with vascular congestion.
4. **Leucocyte accumulation:** as stasis develops, leucocytes, principally neutrophils, accumulate along the endothelium. The endothelial cells are activated and express increased levels of adhesion molecules. Leucocytes then adhere and migrate into the interstitial tissue.

- The vascular reactions are designed to maximise the movement of plasma proteins and leucocytes out of the circulation and into the site of infection or injury.
- Lymphatic vessels also take part. Lymph flow is increased and helps to drain oedema fluid, and the lymphatics and draining lymph nodes may become inflamed (lymphangitis and lymphadenitis).

## Steps of cellular events in acute inflammation.

1. **Margination, rolling and adhesion** of leucocytes to the endothelium in the vessel lumen.
2. **Transmigration (diapedesis)** across the endothelium and the vessel wall.
3. **Chemotaxis:** migration in the tissues toward a chemotactic stimulus.
4. **Leucocyte activation:** recognition of the offending agents by Toll-like receptors and other receptors, which activates the leucocytes.
5. **Phagocytosis and intracellular killing** of the offending agent.

## Steps of the journey of leucocytes from the vessel lumen to the tissue space.

1. **In the vessel lumen: margination, rolling and adhesion to endothelium**

- Blood flow slows (stasis) in the dilated postcapillary venules, and more white cells assume a peripheral position along the endothelium. This is **margination**.
- **Rolling** is mediated by **selectins**: L-selectin on leucocytes, E-selectin on endothelium, and P-selectin on platelets and endothelium. These are low-affinity interactions with a fast off-rate, so the leucocytes bind, detach and bind again.
- TNF and IL-1 induce endothelial expression of E-selectin within 1 to 2 hours. Histamine and thrombin cause P-selectin to move to the cell surface from Weibel-Palade bodies [the granules of endothelial cells].
- **Firm adhesion** is mediated by **integrins** on leucocytes (LFA-1, MAC-1, VLA-4) binding ICAM-1 and VCAM-1 on endothelium. Chemokines activate the rolling leucocytes and convert the integrins to a high-affinity state.

2. **Migration across the endothelium and vessel wall**

- Transmigration, or diapedesis, occurs mainly in postcapillary venules, through interendothelial gaps, toward the chemokines produced at the site of injury.
- CD31 (PECAM-1), present in the intercellular junctions, is involved.
- Leucocytes pierce the basement membrane, probably by secreting collagenases, and enter the extravascular space.

3. **Migration in the tissues toward a chemotactic stimulus**

- Leucocytes move along the chemotactic gradient created by chemokines and other chemoattractants and accumulate at the extravascular site.

## What is chemotaxis?

**Chemotaxis** is locomotion along a chemical gradient.

## Name the chemotactic substances.

**Exogenous**

- Bacterial products, including peptides with N-formylmethionine terminal amino acids and some lipids.

**Endogenous**

- **Cytokines**, particularly those of the chemokine family, for example IL-8.
- **Complement components**, particularly C5a.
- **Arachidonic acid metabolites**, mainly leukotriene B4.

All these agents bind to specific seven-transmembrane G protein-coupled receptors on the surface of leucocytes.

## What are the cellular events of acute inflammation?

The changes in blood flow and vascular permeability are quickly followed by an influx of leucocytes into the tissue. The most important leucocytes are the phagocytes, namely **neutrophils** and **macrophages**.

1. **Margination, rolling and adhesion.** In the dilated venules blood flow slows (stasis), so leucocytes take up a peripheral position along the endothelium. They roll (selectins) and then adhere firmly to the activated endothelium (integrins).
2. **Transmigration (diapedesis).** Leucocytes migrate through the endothelium, mainly in postcapillary venules, and pierce the basement membrane.
3. **Chemotaxis.** Leucocytes migrate in the tissue towards the site of injury along a chemical gradient.
4. **Leucocyte activation.** Recognition of microbes and dead cells by receptors on the leucocytes activates them.
5. **Phagocytosis.** Recognition and attachment, engulfment and formation of a phagocytic vacuole.
6. **Intracellular killing and degradation** of the microbe and the ingested material, by reactive oxygen species, nitric oxide and lysosomal enzymes.

- Neutrophils predominate during the first 6 to 24 hours and are replaced by monocytes and macrophages in 24 to 48 hours.
- Leucocyte products may also be released into the extracellular space and damage normal tissue.

## What are the steps of leukocyte extravasation?

The journey of leucocytes from the vessel lumen to the tissue is a multistep process mediated and controlled by adhesion molecules and cytokines.

1. **Margination.** Dilation of the postcapillary venules slows the blood flow (stasis), and leucocytes move to the periphery along the endothelial surface.
2. **Rolling.** Mediated by **selectins**. E-selectin and P-selectin on the endothelium and L-selectin on leucocytes make low-affinity bonds that are easily broken, so the leucocytes bind, detach and bind again and roll along the endothelium. TNF and IL-1 induce E-selectin within 1 to 2 hours. Histamine and thrombin move P-selectin to the cell surface.
3. **Firm adhesion.** Mediated by **integrins** on leucocytes (LFA-1, MAC-1 and VLA-4) binding to their ligands on the endothelium (**ICAM-1** and **VCAM-1**), which are induced by TNF and IL-1. Chemokines displayed on the endothelium activate the rolling leucocytes and convert the integrins to a high-affinity state. The leucocytes stop rolling and spread out on the endothelial surface.
4. **Transmigration (diapedesis).** Chemokines stimulate the leucocytes to migrate through the interendothelial gaps, mainly in postcapillary venules. **CD31 (PECAM-1)** in the intercellular junctions is involved.
5. **Piercing the basement membrane.** Leucocytes pierce the basement membrane, probably by secreting collagenases, and enter the extravascular space.
6. **Chemotaxis.** The cells then migrate in the tissue towards the chemotactic gradient created by chemokines and other chemoattractants.

## What are phagocytes?

- The two major phagocytes are **neutrophils** and **macrophages**.
- They ingest and destroy bacteria and other microbes, as well as necrotic tissue and foreign substances.
- Phagocytosis and intracellular killing are the functional responses most important for destruction of microbes and other offenders.

## Organs where cells of the MPS are found.

- Connective tissues: macrophages scattered diffusely.
- Liver: **Kupffer cells** [macrophages lining the hepatic sinusoids].
- Spleen and lymph nodes: **sinus histiocytes**.
- Central nervous system: **microglia**.
- Lungs: **alveolar macrophages**.
- Skin and intestinal tract: bone marrow-derived macrophages arise from blood monocytes.

Together these cells comprise the **mononuclear phagocyte system**, also known by the older and inaccurate name reticuloendothelial system.

## Define phagocytosis.

**Phagocytosis** is the process by which leucocytes eliminate microbes and dead cells. The particle is recognised and attached, engulfed into a phagocytic vacuole (phagosome), and then killed and degraded in the phagolysosome.

## Three sequential steps of phagocytosis.

1. **Recognition and attachment** of the particle to be ingested by the leucocyte. Mannose receptors, scavenger receptors and receptors for opsonins (IgG antibodies, the C3b breakdown product of complement, and certain plasma lectins) enable phagocytes to bind microbes.
2. **Engulfment**, with subsequent formation of a phagocytic vacuole. Extensions of the cytoplasm flow around the particle and the plasma membrane pinches off to form a phagosome, which then fuses with a lysosomal granule to form a phagolysosome.
3. **Killing of the microbe and degradation** of the ingested material.

## How is phagocytosed material degraded?

- After engulfment, the phagosome fuses with a lysosomal granule, which discharges its contents into the **phagolysosome**.
- The phagocytosed material is degraded by three groups of agents: **reactive oxygen species (ROS)**, **reactive nitrogen species** derived from nitric oxide (NO), and **lysosomal enzymes**.
- All these killing mechanisms are normally sequestered in lysosomes, to which the phagocytosed material is brought. Potentially lethal molecules are therefore kept away from the cytoplasm and nucleus of the phagocyte.
- Killing and digestion occur most efficiently after **activation** of the phagocytes.

**Reactive oxygen species**

- Phagocyte oxidase (**NADPH oxidase**) converts oxygen to superoxide anion, which is changed to hydrogen peroxide.
- In the azurophilic granules of neutrophils, **myeloperoxidase (MPO)** and a halide such as chloride convert hydrogen peroxide to hypochlorite, which kills microbes by halogenation and by oxidation of proteins and lipids.
- Hydrogen peroxide is also converted to the hydroxyl radical, another powerful destructive agent.

**Nitric oxide**

- NO is produced from arginine by inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS) in activated macrophages, and to a lesser extent in neutrophils.
- In macrophages NO reacts with superoxide to form the highly reactive free radical **peroxynitrite**, which damages the lipids, proteins and nucleic acids of microbes.

**Lysosomal enzymes**

- Neutrophils have smaller specific (secondary) granules (lysozyme, collagenase, gelatinase, lactoferrin) and larger azurophil (primary) granules (MPO, lysozyme, defensins, acid hydrolases, neutral proteases such as elastase).
- Acid proteases degrade bacteria and debris within the phagolysosome, which is acidified by membrane-bound proton pumps.
- Macrophages also contain acid hydrolases, collagenase, elastase, phospholipase and plasminogen activator.

## Outline of the oxygen-dependent mechanism of bacterial killing.

1. **Respiratory burst:** on activation of the phagocyte, the components of **phagocyte oxidase (NADPH oxidase)** assemble in the membrane of the phagosome. The enzyme oxidises NADPH and converts oxygen to **superoxide anion**. In neutrophils this is accompanied by increased oxygen consumption, called the respiratory burst.
2. **Hydrogen peroxide:** superoxide is converted to hydrogen peroxide, mostly by spontaneous dismutation.
3. **Hypochlorite:** hydrogen peroxide is not able to kill microbes efficiently by itself. In the presence of a halide such as chloride, **myeloperoxidase (MPO)** of the azurophilic granules converts it to **hypochlorite**, the active ingredient of household bleach. This is the most potent bactericidal system of neutrophils.
4. **Hydroxyl radical:** hydrogen peroxide is also converted to the hydroxyl radical.
5. **Killing:** hypochlorite destroys microbes by halogenation and by oxidation of proteins and lipids (lipid peroxidation). The oxygen-derived free radicals bind to and modify cellular lipids, proteins and nucleic acids.

- The reactive oxygen species are produced inside the phagolysosome, so they act on the ingested microbe without damaging the host cell.
- Inherited deficiency of the components of phagocyte oxidase causes the immunodeficiency disease **chronic granulomatous disease**.

## Name the cells causing phagocytosis.

- The two major phagocytes are **neutrophils** and **macrophages**.
- They ingest and destroy bacteria and other microbes, as well as necrotic tissue and foreign substances.
- Phagocytosis and intracellular killing are the functional responses most important for destruction of microbes and other offenders.

## What are the steps/mechanism of phagocytosis?

1. **Recognition and attachment** of the particle to be ingested by the leucocyte. Mannose receptors, scavenger receptors and receptors for opsonins (IgG antibodies, the C3b breakdown product of complement, and certain plasma lectins) enable phagocytes to bind microbes.
2. **Engulfment**, with subsequent formation of a phagocytic vacuole. Extensions of the cytoplasm flow around the particle and the plasma membrane pinches off to form a phagosome, which then fuses with a lysosomal granule to form a phagolysosome.
3. **Killing of the microbe and degradation** of the ingested material.

## Write the mechanism of oxygen-dependent microbial killing (role of oxygen in phagocytic killing).

1. **Respiratory burst:** on activation of the phagocyte, the components of **phagocyte oxidase (NADPH oxidase)** assemble in the membrane of the phagosome. The enzyme oxidises NADPH and converts oxygen to **superoxide anion**. In neutrophils this is accompanied by increased oxygen consumption, called the respiratory burst.
2. **Hydrogen peroxide:** superoxide is converted to hydrogen peroxide, mostly by spontaneous dismutation.
3. **Hypochlorite:** hydrogen peroxide is not able to kill microbes efficiently by itself. In the presence of a halide such as chloride, **myeloperoxidase (MPO)** of the azurophilic granules converts it to **hypochlorite**, the active ingredient of household bleach. This is the most potent bactericidal system of neutrophils.
4. **Hydroxyl radical:** hydrogen peroxide is also converted to the hydroxyl radical.
5. **Killing:** hypochlorite destroys microbes by halogenation and by oxidation of proteins and lipids (lipid peroxidation). The oxygen-derived free radicals bind to and modify cellular lipids, proteins and nucleic acids.

- The reactive oxygen species are produced inside the phagolysosome, so they act on the ingested microbe without damaging the host cell.
- Inherited deficiency of the components of phagocyte oxidase causes the immunodeficiency disease **chronic granulomatous disease**.

## Write a short note on opsonin.

**Opsonins** are molecules that coat microbes and greatly enhance the efficiency of phagocytosis. The phagocytes express high-affinity receptors for them.

**The major opsonins**

- **Immunoglobulin G** (IgG) antibodies.
- **C3b**, a breakdown product of complement.
- Certain plasma lectins, notably **mannose-binding lectin** and collectins.

All of these are recognised by specific receptors on leucocytes.

- C3b and its inactive product iC3b fixed to the microbial cell wall promote phagocytosis by neutrophils and macrophages.
- Acute-phase proteins such as C-reactive protein and serum amyloid A bind to microbial cell walls and may act as opsonins and fix complement.

## Name the chemical mediators of inflammation.

Inflammatory mediators are substances that initiate and regulate inflammatory reactions. They are either **cell-derived** or **plasma-derived**.

| Group | Mediators | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **Vasoactive amines** | Histamine, serotonin | Mast cells, basophils, platelets; serotonin also in platelets and neuroendocrine cells |
| **Arachidonic acid metabolites** | Prostaglandins, leukotrienes, lipoxins | Mast cells, leucocytes, platelets, endothelium |
| **Cytokines** | TNF, IL-1, IL-6, IL-17 | Macrophages, dendritic cells, endothelial cells, mast cells, T lymphocytes |
| **Chemokines** | IL-8 (CXCL8), MCP-1, eotaxin and others | Leucocytes, activated macrophages, endothelial cells |
| **Platelet-activating factor** | PAF | Platelets, basophils, mast cells, neutrophils, macrophages, endothelium |
| **Neuropeptides** | Substance P, neurokinin A | Sensory nerves and leucocytes |
| **Complement** | C3a, C5a, C3b, membrane attack complex | Plasma (produced in the liver) |
| **Kinins** | Bradykinin | Plasma (produced in the liver) |

- Cell-derived mediators are stored in granules (histamine) or synthesised de novo (prostaglandins, leukotrienes, cytokines).
- Plasma-derived mediators are present as inactive precursors and are activated by proteolytic cleavage.
- The most important mediators of acute inflammation are the vasoactive amines, lipid products, cytokines and the products of complement activation.

## General principles of action of chemical mediators.

1. The most important mediators of acute inflammation are **vasoactive amines**, **lipid products** (prostaglandins and leukotrienes), **cytokines** (including chemokines) and **products of complement activation**. They induce different components of the response by distinct mechanisms, but there is also some overlap (redundancy) in their actions.
2. Mediators are either **secreted by cells** or **generated from plasma proteins**. Cell-derived mediators are stored in granules and released by exocytosis (histamine), or are synthesised de novo (prostaglandins, leukotrienes, cytokines). Plasma-derived mediators (complement) are made mainly in the liver and circulate as inactive precursors that must be activated by proteolytic cleavage.
3. **Active mediators are produced only in response to offending stimuli,** such as microbial products and substances released from injured or necrotic cells. This ensures that inflammation is triggered only when and where it is needed.
4. **Most mediators are short lived.** They quickly decay, are inactivated by enzymes, or are scavenged or inhibited, which gives a system of checks and balances.
5. **One mediator can stimulate the release of other mediators.** Complement products stimulate histamine release, and TNF acts on endothelial cells to produce IL-1 and chemokines. The secondary mediators may have the same, different or even opposing actions, so such cascades either amplify or counteract the first mediator.

## Principal actions of arachidonic acid metabolites.

Arachidonic acid is released from membrane phospholipids by phospholipase A2. It is converted by **cyclooxygenases** (COX-1 and COX-2) to prostaglandins and by **lipoxygenases** to leukotrienes and lipoxins.

| Action | Eicosanoid |
|---|---|
| **Vasodilation** | Prostaglandins PGI2 (prostacyclin), PGE1, PGE2 and PGD2 |
| **Vasoconstriction** | Thromboxane A2 and leukotrienes C4, D4 and E4 |
| **Increased vascular permeability** | Leukotrienes C4, D4 and E4 |
| **Chemotaxis and leucocyte adhesion** | Leukotriene B4 and HETE |

**Other actions**

- **PGD2** is the major prostaglandin of mast cells; with PGE2 it causes vasodilation and increased permeability of postcapillary venules, which potentiates oedema. PGD2 is also a chemoattractant for neutrophils.
- **Thromboxane A2** (from platelets) is a potent platelet-aggregating agent and vasoconstrictor. **Prostacyclin (PGI2)** (from endothelium) is a vasodilator and a potent inhibitor of platelet aggregation.
- **PGE2** causes pain (it makes the skin hypersensitive to painful stimuli) and fever.
- **Leukotriene B4** is a potent chemotactic agent and activator of neutrophils, causing aggregation and adhesion to venular endothelium, generation of ROS and release of lysosomal enzymes. Leukotrienes C4, D4 and E4 also cause bronchospasm.
- **Lipoxins** suppress inflammation by inhibiting neutrophil chemotaxis and adhesion to endothelium.

## Name some important cytokines in inflammation.

| Cytokine | Principal sources | Principal actions in inflammation |
|---|---|---|
| **In acute inflammation** | | |
| **TNF** | Macrophages, dendritic cells, mast cells, T lymphocytes | Stimulates expression of endothelial adhesion molecules and secretion of other cytokines; systemic effects |
| **IL-1** | Macrophages, dendritic cells, some epithelial cells | Similar to TNF; greater role in fever |
| **IL-6** | Macrophages, other cells | Systemic effects (acute-phase response) |
| **Chemokines** | Macrophages, endothelial cells, T lymphocytes, mast cells | Recruitment of leucocytes to sites of inflammation |
| **IL-17** | T lymphocytes | Recruitment of neutrophils and monocytes |
| **In chronic inflammation** | | |
| **IL-12** | Dendritic cells, macrophages | Increased production of IFN-gamma |
| **IFN-gamma** | T lymphocytes, NK cells | Activation of macrophages, with increased ability to kill microbes and tumour cells |

- TNF and IL-1 are produced mainly by activated macrophages and dendritic cells and play critical roles in leucocyte recruitment.
- All the cytokines of acute inflammation may also contribute to chronic inflammatory reactions.

## Write the functions of vasoactive amines.

The two major vasoactive amines are **histamine** and **serotonin**. They are stored as preformed molecules in cells, so they are among the first mediators to be released in inflammation.

**Histamine**

- The richest source is the **mast cell** of the connective tissue adjacent to blood vessels. It is also found in basophils and platelets.
- It is released by mast cell degranulation in response to physical injury (trauma, cold, heat), binding of antigen to IgE on mast cells (immediate hypersensitivity), and the complement products C3a and C5a (anaphylatoxins). Neuropeptides such as substance P and the cytokines IL-1 and IL-8 may also trigger release.
- Functions: it causes **dilation of arterioles** and **increased permeability of venules**, and is the principal mediator of the immediate transient phase of increased vascular permeability, producing interendothelial gaps in postcapillary venules. It also causes contraction of some smooth muscles.
- Its vascular effects are mediated mainly through **H1 receptors** on microvascular endothelial cells. H1 blockers are used for allergic reactions and H2 blockers reduce gastric acid secretion.

**Serotonin (5-hydroxytryptamine)**

- It is present in platelets and in certain neuroendocrine cells, such as those of the gastrointestinal tract.
- Its primary function is as a neurotransmitter in the gut and central nervous system. It is also a vasoconstrictor, but the importance of this action in inflammation is unclear.

## How is the complement system activated?

Complement proteins (C1 to C9) are present as inactive forms in plasma. Many become proteolytic enzymes that degrade other complement proteins, forming an enzymatic cascade with tremendous amplification. The critical step is the proteolysis of the third and most abundant component, **C3**, which can occur by three pathways.

| Pathway | Trigger | Steps |
|---|---|---|
| **Classical** | C1 binds to antibody (IgM or IgG) combined with antigen | Sequential binding of C2 and C4 forms an enzyme that cleaves C3 |
| **Alternative** | Microbial surface molecules (endotoxin or lipopolysaccharide), complex polysaccharides, cobra venom, without antibody | Spontaneous cleavage of C3 is amplified and stabilised on microbes |
| **Lectin** | Mannose-binding lectin binds carbohydrates on microbes | Activates a protease homologous to C1; later steps as in the classical pathway |

1. All three pathways form **C3 convertase**, which splits C3 into **C3a** (released) and **C3b** (covalently attached to the microbe or cell).
2. More C3b binds to the generated fragments to form **C5 convertase**, which cleaves C5 into **C5a** (released) and **C5b** (attached to the cell surface).
3. C5b binds the late components C6 to C9, forming the **membrane attack complex** (made of multiple C9 molecules).

- The activation is tightly controlled by regulatory proteins: C1 inhibitor, decay accelerating factor (DAF), CD59 and factor H.

## What chemical mediators are responsible for the vascular events/features of acute inflammation?

| Vascular event | Principal mediators |
|---|---|
| **Vasodilation** | **Histamine** (the principal mediator). Prostaglandins (PGI2, PGE2, PGD2), bradykinin, platelet-activating factor, and complement through stimulation of mast cells |
| **Increased vascular permeability** | **Histamine** and **serotonin**. **C3a** and **C5a** (by liberating vasoactive amines from mast cells). **Leukotrienes** C4, D4 and E4. Bradykinin and platelet-activating factor. PGE2 and PGD2 potentiate the oedema |

- Histamine is the principal mediator of the immediate transient phase of increased vascular permeability.
- TNF and IL-1 act on endothelial cells and promote adhesion of leucocytes to the endothelium.

## List the molecules and cytokines involved in inflammation.

**Chemical mediators**

- Vasoactive amines: histamine and serotonin.
- Arachidonic acid metabolites: prostaglandins, leukotrienes and lipoxins.
- Platelet-activating factor.
- Complement: C3a, C5a, C3b and the membrane attack complex.
- Kinins: bradykinin.
- Neuropeptides: substance P and neurokinin A.

**Cytokines**

- In acute inflammation: **TNF**, **IL-1**, **IL-6**, **chemokines** (for example IL-8) and **IL-17**.
- In chronic inflammation: **IL-12** and **IFN-gamma**.

**Adhesion molecules**

- Selectins: L-selectin, E-selectin and P-selectin.
- Integrins: LFA-1, MAC-1 and VLA-4.
- Immunoglobulin family: ICAM-1, VCAM-1 and CD31 (PECAM-1).

## State the consequences of complement activation.

All three pathways of complement activation lead to cleavage of C3. The complement system has three main functions.

1. **Inflammation.** C5a, C3a and, to a lesser extent, C4a stimulate histamine release from mast cells, and so increase vascular permeability and cause vasodilation. They are called **anaphylatoxins**. C5a is also chemotactic for neutrophils, monocytes, eosinophils and basophils, and activates the lipoxygenase pathway of arachidonic acid metabolism in neutrophils and monocytes.
2. **Opsonisation and phagocytosis.** C3b and its inactive product iC3b, fixed to the microbial cell wall, act as opsonins and promote phagocytosis by neutrophils and macrophages, which have receptors for these fragments.
3. **Cell lysis.** Deposition of the **membrane attack complex** makes cells permeable to water and ions and results in osmotic lysis. This is particularly important for killing microbes with thin cell walls, such as Neisseria.

## Which chemical mediators are responsible for pain and swelling?

| Sign | Principal mediators |
|---|---|
| **Pain** | **Prostaglandins** (PGE2 makes the skin hypersensitive to painful stimuli), **bradykinin** and **substance P** |
| **Swelling** (oedema from increased vascular permeability) | **Histamine** and **serotonin**. **C3a** and **C5a**, by liberating vasoactive amines from mast cells. **Leukotrienes** C4, D4 and E4. Bradykinin also increases vascular permeability |

## Morphological types / patterns of acute inflammation.

The morphologic hallmarks of acute inflammation are dilation of small blood vessels and accumulation of leucocytes and fluid in the extravascular tissue. Special patterns are superimposed on these features, depending on the severity of the reaction, its aetiology, and the tissue and site involved. They often give valuable clues about the underlying cause.

| Pattern | Main feature |
|---|---|
| **Serous inflammation** | Exudation of serum-like fluid into spaces created by cell injury, or into body cavities lined by the peritoneum, pleura or pericardium. The fluid typically contains no microbes and few leucocytes. |
| **Fibrinous inflammation** | With a greater increase in vascular permeability, large molecules such as fibrinogen leave the blood and fibrin is deposited in the extracellular space. Typical of the lining of body cavities such as meninges, pericardium and pleura. |
| **Purulent (suppurative) inflammation** | Production of **pus**, an exudate of neutrophils, the liquefied debris of necrotic cells and oedema fluid. Caused mainly by pyogenic (pus-producing) bacteria such as staphylococci. An **abscess** is a localised collection of pus. |
| **Ulcer** | A local defect, or excavation, of the surface of an organ or tissue, produced by sloughing of inflamed necrotic tissue. |

## One example of each morphological type.

| Morphologic type | Example |
|---|---|
| **Serous inflammation** | Skin blister of a burn or viral infection (serous fluid collects within or just beneath the damaged epidermis); effusion into a body cavity |
| **Fibrinous inflammation** | Fibrinous pericarditis (fibrin deposited on the pericardium); also pleura and meninges |
| **Purulent (suppurative) inflammation** | Acute appendicitis |
| **Abscess** | Bacterial abscesses of the lung in bronchopneumonia |
| **Ulcer** | Peptic ulcer of the stomach or duodenum |

## What is an ulcer?

An **ulcer** is a local defect, or excavation, of the surface of an organ or tissue that is produced by the sloughing (shedding) of inflamed necrotic tissue.

## Define abscess.

An **abscess** is a localised collection of pus caused by suppuration buried in a tissue, an organ or a confined space.

## Describe the morphological patterns of acute inflammation.

The morphologic hallmarks of acute inflammation are dilation of small blood vessels and accumulation of leucocytes and fluid in the extravascular tissue. Special patterns are superimposed on these features, depending on the severity of the reaction, its aetiology, and the tissue and site involved. They often give valuable clues about the underlying cause.

| Pattern | Main feature |
|---|---|
| **Serous inflammation** | Exudation of serum-like fluid into spaces created by cell injury, or into body cavities lined by the peritoneum, pleura or pericardium. The fluid typically contains no microbes and few leucocytes. |
| **Fibrinous inflammation** | With a greater increase in vascular permeability, large molecules such as fibrinogen leave the blood and fibrin is deposited in the extracellular space. Typical of the lining of body cavities such as meninges, pericardium and pleura. |
| **Purulent (suppurative) inflammation** | Production of **pus**, an exudate of neutrophils, the liquefied debris of necrotic cells and oedema fluid. Caused mainly by pyogenic (pus-producing) bacteria such as staphylococci. An **abscess** is a localised collection of pus. |
| **Ulcer** | A local defect, or excavation, of the surface of an organ or tissue, produced by sloughing of inflamed necrotic tissue. |

## Beneficial effects of acute inflammation.

- Inflammation is a **protective response that is essential for survival**. It rids the host of both the initial cause of cell injury (microbes, toxins) and its consequences (necrotic cells and tissues).
- It delivers circulating **phagocytic leucocytes, antibodies and complement proteins** from the blood to the site of infection or injury. These are kept in a resting state in the blood and can be rapidly recruited to any site.
- Leucocytes eliminate microbes and dead cells by **phagocytosis**, and neutrophils can form **extracellular traps** that trap and destroy microbes and prevent their spread.
- Fever may be a protective host response.
- Inflammation is followed by **repair**, in which damaged tissue is healed by regeneration and scarring.
- Without inflammation, infections would go unchecked, wounds would never heal, and injured tissues might remain permanent festering sores.

## Harmful effects of acute inflammation.

- **Local tissue damage**: protective reactions to infection are often accompanied by local tissue damage with pain and functional impairment. This is usually self-limited and leaves little or no permanent damage.
- **Leucocyte-mediated injury**: activated leucocytes release enzymes, reactive oxygen species and other products into the extracellular space, which damage normal tissue. This happens as collateral damage in infections, when the response is directed against host tissues (autoimmune diseases), and when there is an excessive reaction to harmless environmental substances (allergic diseases such as asthma).
- **Fluid loss**: increased vascular permeability can cause life-threatening loss of fluid in severely burned patients.
- **Scarring (fibrosis)**: healing by connective tissue replacement, for example organisation of fibrin in the pericardial sac, can obliterate the pericardial space.
- **Progression to chronic inflammation**: the response may become prolonged, with more tissue destruction and fibrosis.
- **Systemic harm**: in sepsis, very high levels of cytokines cause septic shock, with disseminated intravascular coagulation, hypotension and metabolic disturbances.
- Examples of diseases with inflammatory injury: acute respiratory distress syndrome (neutrophils), glomerulonephritis and vasculitis (antibodies, complement, neutrophils and monocytes), and septic shock (cytokines).

## Outcomes of acute inflammation.

Acute inflammation typically has one of three outcomes. The nature and intensity of the injury, the site and tissue affected and the responsiveness of the host all modify the result.

1. **Complete resolution**: the site of acute inflammation is restored to normal.
2. **Healing by connective tissue replacement (scarring, or fibrosis)**.
3. **Progression to chronic inflammation**.

- Resolution is the usual outcome when the injury is limited or short lived, or when there has been little tissue destruction and the damaged parenchymal cells can regenerate.
- Scarring occurs after substantial tissue destruction, when the injury involves tissues that cannot regenerate, or when there is abundant fibrin exudation in tissue or serous cavities (pleura, peritoneum) that cannot be adequately cleared. Connective tissue grows into the area, converting it into fibrous tissue, a process also called organisation.
- Progression to chronic inflammation occurs when the acute response cannot be resolved, either because of persistence of the injurious agent or because of interference with the normal process of healing. An example is acute bacterial infection of the lung progressing to a chronic lung abscess.

## How does (complete) resolution occur?

**Complete resolution** is the restoration of the site of acute inflammation to normal after the offending agent has been eliminated. It is the usual outcome when the injury is limited or short lived, or when there has been little tissue destruction and the damaged parenchymal cells can regenerate.

1. **Removal of the stimulus:** the offending agent is eliminated, mainly by phagocytic cells.
2. **Termination of the acute response:** mediators are produced only while the stimulus persists, have short half-lives and are degraded after release. Neutrophils have a short half-life in tissues and die by apoptosis within hours or a few days. Active stop signals include a switch from proinflammatory leukotrienes to antiinflammatory lipoxins, release of the antiinflammatory cytokines TGF-beta and IL-10, and cholinergic nerve discharge that inhibits TNF production by macrophages.
3. **Removal of cellular debris and microbes** by macrophages.
4. **Resorption of oedema fluid** by lymphatics.
5. **Regeneration** of the damaged tissue.

## What are the systemic effects of inflammation?

Inflammation, even if localised, is associated with systemic reactions induced by cytokines, mainly **TNF, IL-1 and IL-6** (interferons also contribute), whose production is stimulated by bacterial products and other inflammatory stimuli.

1. **Fever**: a rise in body temperature, usually by 1 to 4 degrees Celsius, produced by pyrogens. Exogenous pyrogens (bacterial products such as LPS) stimulate cells to release IL-1 and TNF (endogenous pyrogens), which increase cyclooxygenases and the synthesis of prostaglandins. PGE2 acts on the hypothalamus to reset the body temperature to a higher level. NSAIDs reduce fever by inhibiting prostaglandin synthesis.
2. **Leucocytosis**: the leucocyte count usually rises to 15,000 or 20,000 cells per microlitre, and may reach 40,000 to 100,000 (leukaemoid reaction). It is due to accelerated release of granulocytes from the bone marrow, with a shift to the left, and later to increased production of colony-stimulating factors. Bacterial infections cause neutrophilia, viral infections cause lymphocytosis, and allergies and helminth infestations cause eosinophilia. Some infections (typhoid fever and some viral, rickettsial and protozoal infections) cause leucopenia.
3. **Acute-phase response**: increased synthesis, mainly in the liver, of **C-reactive protein, fibrinogen and serum amyloid A**, stimulated by IL-6, IL-1 and TNF. Fibrinogen causes red cells to form rouleaux, which is the basis of the erythrocyte sedimentation rate. Hepcidin rises and, in chronic inflammation, causes anaemia. Thrombopoietin rises and may cause thrombocytosis.
4. **Other manifestations**: increased pulse and blood pressure, decreased sweating, rigors, chills, anorexia, somnolence and malaise.
5. **Septic shock**: in severe bacterial infection (sepsis), huge amounts of TNF and IL-1 cause disseminated intravascular coagulation, hypotensive shock and metabolic disturbances such as insulin resistance and hyperglycaemia.

## Common investigations usually done in inflammatory conditions.

These are laboratory markers of the systemic response to inflammation.

1. **Total and differential leucocyte count**: leucocytosis is common in inflammation, especially bacterial infection (neutrophilia, with a shift to the left). Viral infections show lymphocytosis, allergies and helminth infestations show eosinophilia, and some infections (typhoid fever, some viral, rickettsial and protozoal infections) show leucopenia.
2. **Erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR)**: fibrinogen makes red cells form stacks (rouleaux) that sediment faster. It is a simple test for an inflammatory response caused by any stimulus.
3. **Acute-phase proteins**: serum **C-reactive protein (CRP)**, fibrinogen and serum amyloid A are raised. CRP is also a marker of increased risk of myocardial infarction in coronary artery disease.
4. **Other blood count changes**: anaemia from hepcidin in chronic inflammation, and thrombocytosis from raised thrombopoietin.

## Define chronic inflammation.

**Chronic inflammation** is a response of prolonged duration (weeks or months) in which inflammation, tissue injury and attempts at repair coexist.

## Causes of chronic inflammation.

1. **Persistent infections** by microorganisms that are difficult to eradicate, such as mycobacteria and certain viruses, fungi and parasites. These often evoke a T lymphocyte reaction called delayed-type hypersensitivity, and some produce a granulomatous reaction. An unresolved acute inflammation may also evolve into chronic inflammation, for example acute bacterial infection of the lung progressing to a chronic lung abscess.
2. **Hypersensitivity diseases**, caused by excessive and inappropriate activation of the immune system.
3. **Prolonged exposure to toxic agents**, either exogenous or endogenous.

- Autoimmune diseases (self antigens evoke a self-perpetuating T and B lymphocyte reaction): rheumatoid arthritis and multiple sclerosis.
- Unregulated immune responses against microbes: inflammatory bowel disease.
- Allergic diseases (immune responses against common environmental substances): bronchial asthma.
- Exogenous toxic agent: particulate silica, a nondegradable inanimate material which, when inhaled in sufficient quantity, causes the inflammatory lung disease **silicosis**.
- Endogenous toxic agent: excess cholesterol and other lipids, which cause **atherosclerosis**, a chronic inflammatory process of the arterial wall.

## Morphological features of chronic inflammation.

In contrast to acute inflammation, which shows vascular changes, oedema and predominantly neutrophilic infiltration, chronic inflammation is characterised by:

1. **Infiltration with mononuclear cells**, which include macrophages, lymphocytes and plasma cells.
2. **Tissue destruction**, induced by the persistent offending agent or by the inflammatory cells.
3. **Attempts at healing** by connective tissue replacement of damaged tissue, accomplished by **angiogenesis** (proliferation of small blood vessels) and, in particular, **fibrosis**.

- In the lung, chronic inflammation shows a collection of chronic inflammatory cells, destruction of the parenchyma (normal alveoli replaced by spaces lined by cuboidal epithelium), and replacement by connective tissue (fibrosis).
- In acute inflammation of the lung (acute bronchopneumonia), neutrophils fill the alveolar spaces and the blood vessels are congested.

## Common examples of chronic inflammation.

| Cause | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Persistent infections** | Tuberculosis (prototype of a granulomatous disease), leprosy, syphilis, cat-scratch disease, chronic bacterial infection of bone (osteomyelitis) |
| **Autoimmune diseases** | Rheumatoid arthritis, multiple sclerosis, Hashimoto thyroiditis [autoimmune thyroiditis causing hypothyroidism] |
| **Unregulated immune response against microbes or self antigens** | Inflammatory bowel disease (Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease]), sarcoidosis (cause unknown) |
| **Allergic diseases** | Bronchial asthma |
| **Toxic substances** | Silicosis (inhaled silica), atherosclerosis (cholesterol and other lipids) |
| **Unresolved acute inflammation** | Acute bacterial infection of the lung progressing to a chronic lung abscess; peptic ulcer, in which acute and chronic inflammation coexist |

## Macrophage is the central cell of chronic inflammation / role of macrophage in chronic inflammation.

**Macrophages** are the dominant cells in most chronic inflammatory reactions. They are professional phagocytes that eliminate microbes and damaged tissues and also play important roles in the repair of injured tissue.

**Origin and distribution**

- They are derived from haematopoietic stem cells of the bone marrow (circulating monocytes, half-life about 1 day) and, in early development, from progenitors in the yolk sac and foetal liver.
- Tissue macrophages live for several months or years. They are present in specific locations: **Kupffer cells** [macrophages lining the hepatic sinusoids] in the liver, **sinus histiocytes** in the spleen and lymph nodes, **microglia** in the central nervous system and **alveolar macrophages** in the lungs. Together they form the mononuclear phagocyte system.
- Monocytes begin to emigrate into the tissues early in inflammation, and within 48 hours they may be the predominant cell type.

**Role in chronic inflammation**

1. They ingest and destroy microbes and dead tissues.
2. They initiate tissue repair and are involved in scar formation and fibrosis.
3. They secrete mediators of inflammation, such as TNF, IL-1, chemokines and eicosanoids, and so initiate and propagate the reaction.
4. They display antigens to T lymphocytes and respond to signals from T cells, setting up a feedback loop that sustains chronic inflammation.

**Pathways of activation**

- **Classical (M1)** activation is induced by microbial products (endotoxin) and by IFN-gamma. These macrophages produce NO, ROS and lysosomal enzymes, which kill microbes, and they secrete cytokines that promote inflammation.
- **Alternative (M2)** activation is induced by IL-4 and IL-13. These macrophages are not microbicidal; they terminate inflammation and promote tissue repair.
- The same products that make macrophages powerful defenders also cause the tissue destruction that is a hallmark of chronic inflammation.

## Define granuloma with an example.

**Granulomatous inflammation** is a form of chronic inflammation characterised by collections of activated macrophages, often with T lymphocytes, and sometimes associated with necrosis. A **granuloma** is such a focus of inflammation.

**Example:** tuberculosis, the prototype of a granulomatous disease caused by infection. The granulomas are called **tubercles**, with central caseous necrosis surrounded by epithelioid cells, giant cells and lymphocytes.

## How is a granuloma formed?

**Granuloma formation** is a cellular attempt to contain an offending agent that is difficult to eradicate.

1. A persistent microbe or other chronic stimulus activates **T lymphocytes**.
2. **Macrophages** display the antigen to T cells and produce IL-12, which stimulates the T-cell response.
3. **Th1 cells** produce cytokines such as **IFN-gamma**, which activates the macrophages. Activated T cells also produce TNF, IL-17 and chemokines that recruit more macrophages.
4. Activated macrophages produce cytokines (IL-12 and others) that stimulate the T cells again. This cycle of T cell and macrophage reactions sustains the chronic inflammation.
5. The activated macrophages develop abundant cytoplasm and resemble epithelial cells (**epithelioid cells**). Some of them fuse to form **multinucleate giant cells**.
6. The aggregate of epithelioid cells is surrounded by a collar of lymphocytes. In some infections (classically Mycobacterium tuberculosis), hypoxia and free radical injury lead to a central zone of caseous necrosis.
7. Older granulomas develop a rim of fibroblasts and connective tissue, and healing is accompanied by fibrosis.

- In foreign body granulomas no T-cell immune response is involved; the inert foreign material itself induces the reaction.

## Types of granuloma.

There are two types of **granuloma**, which differ in their pathogenesis.

| Feature | Foreign body granuloma | Immune granuloma |
|---|---|---|
| **Cause** | Inert foreign bodies, which induce inflammation in the absence of a T cell-mediated immune response | Agents that induce a persistent T cell-mediated immune response, such as a persistent microbe |
| **Mechanism** | The material is large enough to preclude phagocytosis by macrophages and is not immunogenic | Th1 cells produce IFN-gamma, which activates macrophages. In some parasitic infections (schistosomiasis) there are strong Th2 responses and eosinophils |
| **Examples** | Talc (in intravenous drug use), suture material, other fibres | Tuberculosis, leprosy, sarcoidosis, Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease] |
