| **Morphology** | Epithelioid cells and giant cells are apposed to the surface of the foreign body, which is often seen in the centre, sometimes inside giant cells, refractile in polarised light | Epithelioid cells with a collar of lymphocytes; may be caseating or noncaseating |

- Caseating granulomas have a central zone of caseous necrosis (tuberculosis). Noncaseating granulomas lack necrotic centres (Crohn disease, sarcoidosis, foreign body reactions).

## Examples of granulomatous inflammation.

| Disease | Aetiology | Tissue reaction |
|---|---|---|
| **Tuberculosis** | Mycobacterium tuberculosis | Caseating granulomas (tubercles): activated macrophages (epithelioid cells) rimmed by fibroblasts, lymphocytes and histiocytes, occasional Langhans giant cells [giant cells with peripheral horseshoe-shaped nuclei], central necrosis with amorphous granular debris, acid-fast bacilli |
| **Leprosy** | Mycobacterium leprae | Acid-fast bacilli in macrophages; noncaseating granulomas |
| **Syphilis** | Treponema pallidum | Gumma: microscopic to grossly visible lesion with an enclosing wall of histiocytes, plasma cell infiltrate, central cells necrotic without loss of cellular outline |
| **Cat-scratch disease** | Bartonella henselae (a gram-negative bacillus) | Rounded or stellate granuloma with central granular debris and recognisable neutrophils; giant cells uncommon |
| **Sarcoidosis** | Unknown | Noncaseating granulomas with abundant activated macrophages |
| **Crohn disease** [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease] | Immune reaction against intestinal bacteria, possibly self antigens | Occasional noncaseating granulomas in the wall of the intestine, with a dense chronic inflammatory infiltrate |

- Foreign body granulomas also occur around talc, suture material and other fibres.
- Schistosomiasis produces granulomas with strong Th2 responses and eosinophils.

## Morphological features of chronic granulomatous inflammation.

1. In the usual H&E preparations the activated macrophages have **pink granular cytoplasm with indistinct cell boundaries** and are called **epithelioid cells**.
2. The aggregates of epithelioid macrophages are surrounded by a **collar of lymphocytes**.
3. Older granulomas may have a **rim of fibroblasts and connective tissue**.
4. Frequently, but not invariably, **multinucleated giant cells** 40 to 50 micrometres in diameter are found. These are called **Langhans giant cells** [giant cells with peripheral horseshoe-shaped nuclei]. They consist of a large mass of cytoplasm and many nuclei and derive from the fusion of multiple activated macrophages.
5. In granulomas associated with certain infectious organisms (most classically Mycobacterium tuberculosis), hypoxia and free radical-mediated injury lead to a **central zone of caseous necrosis**. Grossly it is granular and cheesy. Microscopically it is amorphous, structureless, eosinophilic, granular debris with complete loss of cellular detail.
6. The granulomas of Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease], sarcoidosis and foreign body reactions tend to lack necrotic centres and are called noncaseating.
7. Healing of granulomas is accompanied by **fibrosis**, which may be extensive in involved organs.

## Write a short note on granulomatous inflammation.

**Granulomatous inflammation** is a form of chronic inflammation characterised by collections of activated macrophages, often with T lymphocytes, and sometimes associated with necrosis. A **granuloma** is a cellular attempt to contain an offending agent that is difficult to eradicate.

**Types**

1. **Foreign body granulomas**: reactions to inert foreign bodies (talc, suture material, fibres) in the absence of a T cell-mediated immune response.
2. **Immune granulomas**: caused by agents that induce a persistent T cell-mediated immune response, in which Th1 cells produce IFN-gamma, which activates macrophages.

**Morphology**

- Activated macrophages with pink granular cytoplasm and indistinct cell boundaries are called **epithelioid cells**. They are surrounded by a collar of lymphocytes. Older granulomas have a rim of fibroblasts and connective tissue.
- Frequently there are **multinucleate giant cells** (Langhans giant cells [giant cells with peripheral horseshoe-shaped nuclei]), 40 to 50 micrometres in diameter, formed by the fusion of activated macrophages.
- In tuberculosis a central zone of caseous necrosis develops. Granulomas of Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease], sarcoidosis and foreign body reactions are noncaseating.
- Healing is accompanied by fibrosis, which may be extensive.

**Examples:** tuberculosis (the prototype, with granulomas called tubercles), leprosy, syphilis, cat-scratch disease, sarcoidosis and Crohn disease.

## Mention the types/etiological classification of granuloma with examples.

There are two types of **granuloma**, which differ in their pathogenesis.

| Feature | Foreign body granuloma | Immune granuloma |
|---|---|---|
| **Cause** | Inert foreign bodies, which induce inflammation in the absence of a T cell-mediated immune response | Agents that induce a persistent T cell-mediated immune response, such as a persistent microbe |
| **Mechanism** | The material is large enough to preclude phagocytosis by macrophages and is not immunogenic | Th1 cells produce IFN-gamma, which activates macrophages. In some parasitic infections (schistosomiasis) there are strong Th2 responses and eosinophils |
| **Examples** | Talc (in intravenous drug use), suture material, other fibres | Tuberculosis, leprosy, sarcoidosis, Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease] |
| **Morphology** | Epithelioid cells and giant cells are apposed to the surface of the foreign body, which is often seen in the centre, sometimes inside giant cells, refractile in polarised light | Epithelioid cells with a collar of lymphocytes; may be caseating or noncaseating |

- Caseating granulomas have a central zone of caseous necrosis (tuberculosis). Noncaseating granulomas lack necrotic centres (Crohn disease, sarcoidosis, foreign body reactions).

## Draw and label a tubercular (typical) granuloma.

A typical tuberculous granuloma (**tubercle**) is a rounded focus with the following parts, from the centre outwards.

1. **Central caseous necrosis.** Amorphous, structureless, eosinophilic granular debris, with complete loss of cellular detail. Acid-fast bacilli can be seen.
2. **Epithelioid cells.** Activated macrophages with pink granular cytoplasm and indistinct cell boundaries, around the necrosis.
3. **Langhans giant cells [giant cells with peripheral horseshoe-shaped nuclei].** Multinucleate cells, 40 to 50 micrometres in diameter, formed by fusion of macrophages.
4. **Collar of lymphocytes** around the epithelioid cells.
5. **Rim of fibroblasts and connective tissue**, in older granulomas.

- In the diagram, label the area of central necrosis, surrounded by multiple giant cells, epithelioid cells and lymphocytes.

## How does immune granuloma form?

**Granuloma formation** is a cellular attempt to contain an offending agent that is difficult to eradicate.

1. A persistent microbe or other chronic stimulus activates **T lymphocytes**.
2. **Macrophages** display the antigen to T cells and produce IL-12, which stimulates the T-cell response.
3. **Th1 cells** produce cytokines such as **IFN-gamma**, which activates the macrophages. Activated T cells also produce TNF, IL-17 and chemokines that recruit more macrophages.
4. Activated macrophages produce cytokines (IL-12 and others) that stimulate the T cells again. This cycle of T cell and macrophage reactions sustains the chronic inflammation.
5. The activated macrophages develop abundant cytoplasm and resemble epithelial cells (**epithelioid cells**). Some of them fuse to form **multinucleate giant cells**.
6. The aggregate of epithelioid cells is surrounded by a collar of lymphocytes. In some infections (classically Mycobacterium tuberculosis), hypoxia and free radical injury lead to a central zone of caseous necrosis.
7. Older granulomas develop a rim of fibroblasts and connective tissue, and healing is accompanied by fibrosis.

- In foreign body granulomas no T-cell immune response is involved; the inert foreign material itself induces the reaction.

## Give the immunopathogenesis of TB granuloma.

Immunity to Mycobacterium tuberculosis is mediated by **Th1 cells**, which stimulate macrophages to kill the bacteria. The same response produces the granuloma and the tissue damage.

1. **Entry into macrophages.** The bacteria are phagocytosed by macrophages, mainly alveolar macrophages.
2. **Replication in macrophages.** The bacteria block formation of the phagolysosome, so they multiply unchecked in the phagosome. In the first 3 weeks, in the nonsensitised person, the bacteria proliferate in alveolar macrophages and air spaces and seed multiple sites through the blood.
3. **Th1 response.** About 3 weeks after infection, dendritic cells carry the antigens to the draining lymph nodes and present them to T cells. **IL-12** and **IL-18**, produced by antigen-presenting cells, drive differentiation of Th1 cells.
4. **Macrophage activation.** Th1 cells produce **IFN-gamma**, which activates macrophages to produce nitric oxide and reactive oxygen species in the phagolysosome, mobilises defensins such as cathelicidin, and stimulates autophagy. The macrophages become bactericidal.
5. **Granuloma formation.** The Th1 response orchestrates formation of granulomas. Macrophages activated by IFN-gamma become **epithelioid cells**, and some fuse to form **giant cells**. Activated macrophages secrete **TNF** and chemokines, which recruit more monocytes.
6. **Caseous necrosis.** If the infection progresses, the ongoing immune response causes caseous necrosis in the centre of the granuloma.

- Host susceptibility to disease is increased by AIDS (loss of Th1 function), TNF inhibitors and other forms of immunosuppression.

## Define a giant cell.

A **giant cell** (multinucleate giant cell) is a large mass of cytoplasm containing many nuclei, which derives from the fusion of multiple activated macrophages.

## Names / types of inflammatory giant cells with examples.

| Type | Where seen | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Langhans giant cell** [giant cells with peripheral horseshoe-shaped nuclei] | Immune granulomas, classically tuberculosis; also sarcoidosis | 40 to 50 micrometres in diameter; large mass of cytoplasm with many nuclei arranged in a horseshoe or ring at the periphery |
| **Foreign body giant cell** | Foreign body granulomas around talc, suture material and other fibres | Many nuclei scattered haphazardly through the cytoplasm; the giant cells are apposed to the surface of the foreign body, and the material may lie inside them |
| **Touton giant cell** [giant cells with a wreath of nuclei] | Lesions rich in lipid, such as xanthoma and fat necrosis | Ring of nuclei around a central eosinophilic area, with foamy (lipid-filled) cytoplasm at the periphery |
| **Warthin-Finkeldey cell** [multinucleated giant cells of measles] | Lymphoid organs, lung and sputum in measles | Multinucleate giant cell with eosinophilic nuclear and cytoplasmic inclusion bodies; pathognomonic of measles |


## How are inflammatory giant cells formed?

**Inflammatory giant cells** are formed by the **fusion of activated macrophages**.

1. A persistent stimulus that cannot be removed (a microbe such as Mycobacterium tuberculosis, or a foreign body) leads to continuous recruitment and activation of macrophages.
2. In immune granulomas, a cycle between T cells and macrophages is set up. Th1 cells produce IFN-gamma, which activates the macrophages, and the macrophages stimulate T cells through antigen presentation and IL-12.
3. The activated macrophages develop abundant cytoplasm and resemble epithelial cells (epithelioid cells).
4. Some of these activated macrophages **fuse together**, forming a multinucleate giant cell with a large mass of cytoplasm and many nuclei.

- In foreign body granulomas the material is large enough to preclude phagocytosis by macrophages and is not immunogenic, so there is no specific T cell response. The macrophages and giant cells line up along the surface of the foreign body.

## Define regeneration.

**Regeneration** is the process by which some tissues replace the damaged components and essentially return to a normal state.

## Define repair.

**Repair**, also called **healing**, refers to the restoration of tissue architecture and function after an injury.

## What is healing?

**Repair**, also called **healing**, refers to the restoration of tissue architecture and function after an injury.

## Types of cells on the basis of regenerating capability, with examples.

Tissues are divided into three groups, based on their intrinsic proliferative capacity.

| Type | Features | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Labile (continuously dividing) cells** | Continuously lost and replaced by maturation from tissue stem cells and proliferation of mature cells. They regenerate readily after injury as long as the pool of stem cells is preserved. | Haematopoietic cells of the bone marrow; surface epithelia such as the stratified squamous epithelium of the skin, oral cavity, oesophagus, vagina and cervix; cuboidal epithelium of ducts of exocrine organs (salivary glands, pancreas, biliary tract); columnar epithelium of the gastrointestinal tract, uterus and fallopian tubes; transitional epithelium of the urinary tract |
| **Stable (quiescent) cells** | In the G0 stage of the cell cycle with minimal proliferative activity normally, but they can divide in response to injury or loss of tissue mass. Apart from the liver, they have a limited capacity to regenerate. | Parenchyma of most solid organs (liver, kidney, pancreas); endothelial cells, fibroblasts and smooth muscle cells |
| **Permanent (nondividing) cells** | Terminally differentiated and nonproliferative in postnatal life. Injury is irreversible and heals by scar. | Most neurons and cardiac muscle cells (skeletal muscle is usually classified here, but satellite cells give some regenerative capacity) |

## What do you mean by regeneration and repair?

**Repair** (healing) refers to the restoration of tissue architecture and function after an injury. It occurs by two processes.

1. **Regeneration** restores normal cells. Some tissues replace the damaged components and essentially return to a normal state. It occurs by proliferation of differentiated cells that survive the injury and retain the capacity to proliferate (for example hepatocytes), or by tissue stem cells and their progenitors (for example the epithelia of skin and intestine).
2. **Scarring** is the deposition of connective tissue. It occurs when the injured tissue is incapable of complete restitution or when the supporting structures are damaged. The fibrous scar is not normal but gives the tissue enough structural stability to function.

## What is granulation tissue?

**Granulation tissue** is the tissue formed by migration and proliferation of fibroblasts and deposition of loose connective tissue, together with the vessels and interspersed mononuclear leucocytes (not to be confused with granuloma).

## Morphological features of granulation tissue.

**Gross appearance**

- The term granulation tissue derives from its **pink, soft, granular** appearance, as seen beneath the scab of a skin wound.

**Microscopic appearance**

- **Proliferation of fibroblasts** and **new thin-walled, delicate capillaries** (angiogenesis), in a **loose extracellular matrix**.
- Admixed inflammatory cells, mainly **macrophages**.
- Oedema is seen, and little mature collagen is present at this stage.

- Granulation tissue progressively fills the site of injury. The amount formed depends on the size of the tissue defect and the intensity of inflammation.
- It is progressively replaced by collagen, and with maturation of the scar it changes from a highly vascular tissue into a pale, largely avascular scar.

## Define wound healing.

**Wound healing** is the repair of tissue after an injury, that is, the restoration of tissue architecture and function. Healing of skin wounds shows both mechanisms of repair: regeneration, which restores normal cells, and scarring, the deposition of connective tissue.

## Classify wound healing.

Skin **wound healing** is classified into two types, depending on the extent of tissue injury and the dominant mechanism of healing.

| Feature | Healing by first intention (primary union) | Healing by second intention (secondary union) |
|---|---|---|
| **Type of wound** | Well-apposed surgical incisions | Larger wounds, especially if infected |
| **Mechanism** | Epithelial regeneration | Combination of regeneration and scarring |
| **Scarring** | Minimal | More extensive scarring, with wound contraction |

- **The steps** in both types are inflammation, formation of granulation tissue and remodelling of the extracellular matrix (scar formation).

## Factors that influence wound healing / tissue repair.

Repair may be altered by factors that are **extrinsic** or **intrinsic** to the injured tissue, and **systemic** or **local**.

1. **Infection**: clinically one of the most important causes of delayed healing; it prolongs inflammation and may increase local tissue injury.
2. **Diabetes**: one of the most important systemic causes of abnormal wound healing.
3. **Nutritional status**: protein deficiency and vitamin C deficiency inhibit collagen synthesis and retard healing.
4. **Glucocorticoids (steroids)**: antiinflammatory; they may weaken the scar by inhibiting TGF-beta production and diminishing fibrosis.
5. **Ageing**: reduces the capacity to heal injured tissue.
6. **Mechanical factors**: excessive mobility prevents apposition of wound edges (fractures, skin grafts); increased local pressure or torsion may pull wounds apart.
7. **Poor perfusion**: peripheral vascular disease, arteriosclerosis, diabetes and obstructed venous drainage (varicose veins).
8. **Foreign bodies**: fragments of steel, glass or bone impede healing by perpetuating chronic inflammation.
9. **Type and extent of the injury and the tissue involved**: complete restoration occurs only in tissues of stable and labile cells; injury to tissues of permanent cells always results in scarring and some loss of function.
10. **Location of the injury**: inflammation in tissue spaces (pleural, peritoneal, synovial) produces extensive exudate. It may resolve by digestion and resorption, but with larger accumulations granulation tissue grows into the exudate and a scar forms (organisation).

## Mention the complications of wound healing.

Complications of repair arise from abnormalities in any of the basic components of the process: deficient scar formation, excessive formation of the repair components and formation of contractures.

1. **Defective healing**
2. **Excessive scarring**
3. **Contracture**

- **Wound dehiscence:** a surgical incision reopens, internally or externally. Risk factors are obesity, malnutrition, infection, vascular insufficiency and mechanical factors; in abdominal wounds it can be precipitated by vomiting and coughing.
- **Chronic wounds:** venous leg ulcers (chronic venous hypertension), arterial ulcers (atherosclerosis of peripheral arteries, especially in diabetes), diabetic ulcers (ischaemia, neuropathy and secondary infection) and pressure sores (prolonged compression of tissue against bone).
- **Hypertrophic scar:** a raised scar from excess collagen, rich in myofibroblasts; it tends to regress over several months.
- **Keloid:** scar tissue that grows beyond the boundaries of the original wound and does not regress; it seems to be an individual predisposition.
- **Exuberant granulation (proud flesh):** excess granulation tissue protruding above the surrounding skin and blocking re-epithelialisation; it must be removed by cautery or surgical excision.
- **Desmoids (aggressive fibromatoses):** rarely, exuberant proliferation of fibroblasts follows incisional scars or traumatic injury and may recur after excision.
- **Contracture:** exaggerated wound contraction, leading to deformities of the wound and surrounding tissue. It occurs on the palms, soles and anterior thorax, and after serious burns it can compromise joint movement.

## How does diabetes mellitus impair wound healing?

Diabetes is a metabolic disease that compromises tissue repair for many reasons and is one of the most important systemic causes of abnormal wound healing.

1. **Poor perfusion**: diabetes causes accelerated atherosclerosis of the aorta and the large and medium arteries and microangiopathy (thickening of capillary basement membranes). The result is ischaemia, with poor delivery of oxygen and nutrients to the wound. Gangrene of the lower extremities is about 100 times more common in people with diabetes.
2. **Neuropathy**: distal symmetric polyneuropathy of the lower limbs, so injuries to the feet go unnoticed and the wounds are repeatedly traumatised.
3. **Infection**: patients have enhanced susceptibility to skin infections. Infection prolongs inflammation, increases local tissue injury and delays healing.

- The outcome is the **diabetic ulcer** of the lower extremity, particularly the foot. There is tissue necrosis and failure to heal because of vascular disease causing ischaemia, neuropathy and secondary infection. Histologically there is epithelial ulceration with extensive granulation tissue in the underlying dermis.

## What is angiogenesis?

**Angiogenesis** is the process of new blood vessel development from existing vessels.

It is critical in healing at sites of injury, in the development of collateral circulation at sites of ischaemia, and in allowing tumours to increase in size beyond the limits of their original blood supply.

**Steps** (sprouting of new vessels from existing ones)

1. Vasodilation in response to nitric oxide, and increased permeability induced by vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF).
2. Separation of pericytes from the abluminal surface and breakdown of the basement membrane, to allow formation of a vessel sprout.
3. Migration of endothelial cells towards the area of tissue injury.
4. Proliferation of endothelial cells just behind the leading (tip) cell of the migrating cells.
5. Remodelling into capillary tubes.
6. Recruitment of periendothelial cells (pericytes for small capillaries and smooth muscle cells for larger vessels) to form the mature vessel.
7. Suppression of endothelial proliferation and migration, and deposition of the basement membrane.

**Mediators**

- **VEGF**, mainly VEGF-A, stimulates migration and proliferation of endothelial cells (the angiogenic switch) and promotes vasodilation by stimulating nitric oxide.
- **FGF-2** stimulates proliferation of endothelial cells.
- **Angiopoietins 1 and 2**, **PDGF** and **TGF-beta** stabilise the new vessels. PDGF recruits smooth muscle cells, and TGF-beta suppresses endothelial proliferation and enhances production of extracellular matrix proteins.
- **Matrix metalloproteinases** degrade the extracellular matrix to permit extension of the vascular tube.

## Mention the factors influencing/delaying wound healing.

Repair may be altered by factors that are **extrinsic** or **intrinsic** to the injured tissue, and **systemic** or **local**.

1. **Infection**: clinically one of the most important causes of delayed healing; it prolongs inflammation and may increase local tissue injury.
2. **Diabetes**: one of the most important systemic causes of abnormal wound healing.
3. **Nutritional status**: protein deficiency and vitamin C deficiency inhibit collagen synthesis and retard healing.
4. **Glucocorticoids (steroids)**: antiinflammatory; they may weaken the scar by inhibiting TGF-beta production and diminishing fibrosis.
5. **Ageing**: reduces the capacity to heal injured tissue.
6. **Mechanical factors**: excessive mobility prevents apposition of wound edges (fractures, skin grafts); increased local pressure or torsion may pull wounds apart.
7. **Poor perfusion**: peripheral vascular disease, arteriosclerosis, diabetes and obstructed venous drainage (varicose veins).
8. **Foreign bodies**: fragments of steel, glass or bone impede healing by perpetuating chronic inflammation.
9. **Type and extent of the injury and the tissue involved**: complete restoration occurs only in tissues of stable and labile cells; injury to tissues of permanent cells always results in scarring and some loss of function.
10. **Location of the injury**: inflammation in tissue spaces (pleural, peritoneal, synovial) produces extensive exudate. It may resolve by digestion and resorption, but with larger accumulations granulation tissue grows into the exudate and a scar forms (organisation).

## Mention the sequential steps of healing by first intention (primary union).

**Healing by first intention** (primary union) occurs when there is minimal scarring and restitution is by epithelial regeneration. It is typically seen in well-apposed surgical incisions. It follows the steps of repair by scarring.

1. **Haemostasis (within minutes).** A haemostatic plug of platelets forms, stops the bleeding and provides a scaffold for a stable clot.
2. **Inflammation (6 to 48 hours).** Neutrophils and then monocytes are recruited to eliminate the offending agents and clear the debris. The inflammation resolves as these are cleared.
3. **Cell proliferation (up to 10 days).** Epithelial cells, endothelial and other vascular cells, and fibroblasts proliferate and migrate to close the clean wound.

- Epithelial cells respond to locally produced growth factors and migrate over the wound to cover it.
- Endothelial cells proliferate to form new vessels (angiogenesis).
- Fibroblasts migrate into the site and lay down collagen fibres.

4. **Formation of granulation tissue.** Fibroblasts, loose connective tissue, new capillaries and macrophages fill the site of injury. Because the gap is small, only a little granulation tissue forms.
5. **Deposition of connective tissue and remodelling.** Granulation tissue is progressively replaced by collagen, leading to a stable fibrous scar. The scar then becomes largely avascular and is remodelled by matrix metalloproteinases.

## Write a short note on keloid.

A **keloid** is a raised scar in which the scar tissue grows beyond the boundaries of the original wound and does not regress.

- Excessive formation of the components of the repair process can give rise to hypertrophic scars and keloids. Both are raised scars caused by accumulation of excessive amounts of collagen.
- **Hypertrophic scars** often grow rapidly and contain abundant myofibroblasts, but they tend to regress over several months. They generally develop after thermal or traumatic injury that involves the deep layers of the dermis.
- Keloid formation seems to be an individual predisposition. Keloids may appear erythematous in light skin tones or hyperpigmented in darker skin.
- Microscopically there is thick connective tissue deposited in the dermis.
- Rarely, incisional scars or traumatic injuries are followed by exuberant proliferation of fibroblasts that may recur after excision. Desmoids (aggressive fibromatoses) are benign neoplasms of connective tissue.

## A 30y African-American lady has a raised scar larger than the incision, recurring after excision. What is your probable diagnosis?

The probable diagnosis is a **keloid**.

- The scar is raised and grows beyond the boundaries of the original wound (larger than the incision). A hypertrophic scar stays within the wound and tends to regress over several months.
- It does not regress and it recurs after excision.
- Keloid formation seems to be an individual predisposition, and keloids appear hyperpigmented in darker skin.
- Microscopically, thick connective tissue is deposited in the dermis.

## Painful, red, warm swelling (leg in a 15-year-old girl) / pain and swelling in the right inguinal region of a 10-year-old boy for 7 days — Likely diagnosis? Chemical mediators responsible for the signs (pain and swelling)?

**Acute inflammation** is the likely diagnosis in both children. Pain, redness, warmth and swelling are the cardinal signs of acute inflammation. The mediators of pain are prostaglandins, bradykinin and substance P, and the mediators of swelling are histamine, serotonin, C3a and C5a, and leukotrienes.

**Likely diagnosis**

- **15-year-old girl, leg:** acute inflammation of the leg, most probably purulent (suppurative) inflammation due to pyogenic bacteria such as staphylococci, which may form an abscess.
- **10-year-old boy, right inguinal region, 7 days:** acute inflammation of the inguinal lymph nodes (lymphadenitis), secondary to infection in the area drained. The nodes are enlarged because of hyperplasia of the lymphoid follicles and increased numbers of lymphocytes and macrophages. Red streaks on the skin represent inflamed lymphatic channels (lymphangitis).

**Mediators**

| Sign | Principal mediators |
|---|---|
| **Pain** | Prostaglandins (PGE2 makes the skin hypersensitive to painful stimuli), bradykinin and substance P |
| **Swelling** (oedema from increased vascular permeability) | Histamine and serotonin. C3a and C5a, by liberating vasoactive amines from mast cells. Leukotrienes C4, D4 and E4. Bradykinin also increases vascular permeability |

## 8-year-old boy with right iliac fossa pain for 2 days / 15-year-old boy with RIF pain, rebound tenderness and fever — Most probable diagnosis? Expected histological findings? Positive haematological findings expected?

The most probable diagnosis is **acute appendicitis**. The diagnosis on histology requires neutrophilic infiltration of the muscularis propria, and the blood shows neutrophilic leucocytosis.

**Diagnosis**

- Acute appendicitis is most common in children and adolescents.
- It is initiated by increased pressure in the lumen, which compromises venous outflow. Obstruction of the lumen, usually by a faecolith, is found in 50% to 80% of cases. Stasis of luminal contents allows bacteria to proliferate, which leads to ischaemia and inflammation.
- Typically there is periumbilical pain that localises to the right lower quadrant, followed by nausea, vomiting and low-grade fever.

**Expected histological findings**

- **Early:** congested subserosal vessels, with a modest perivascular neutrophilic infiltrate in all layers of the wall. Mucosal neutrophils and focal superficial ulceration are often present but are not specific.
- **Diagnostic:** neutrophilic infiltration of the muscularis propria.
- **More severe:** a prominent neutrophilic exudate that produces a serosal fibrinopurulent reaction. Focal abscesses may form in the wall (acute suppurative appendicitis).
- **Advanced:** ischaemia, haemorrhagic ulceration and gangrenous necrosis (acute gangrenous appendicitis), which may be followed by rupture and suppurative peritonitis.

**Haematological findings**

- Neutrophilic leucocytosis, as in other causes of acute inflammation. The count usually rises to 15,000 or 20,000 per microlitre, with a shift to the left.
- Raised acute-phase proteins, such as C-reactive protein, and a raised erythrocyte sedimentation rate.

## Discharging sinus in the shin of the leg with radiological involucrum (sclerosis) — Most probable diagnosis?

The most probable diagnosis is **chronic osteomyelitis** of the tibia, a pyogenic infection of bone in which the draining sinus and the involucrum are characteristic.

- Pyogenic osteomyelitis is almost always caused by bacterial infection, and Staphylococcus aureus is responsible for 80% to 90% of culture-positive cases.
- In the acute phase the bacteria induce a neutrophilic reaction, and necrosis of bone cells and marrow follows within 48 hours. The infection spreads to the periosteum, and soft tissue abscesses may form and channel to the skin as **draining sinuses**.
- The dead bone is called the **sequestrum**.
- The new reactive bone forms a shell of living tissue around the infected dead bone, called the **involucrum**. This is the sclerosis seen on the radiograph.
- The histological findings of chronic osteomyelitis are marrow fibrosis, sequestrum and a chronic inflammatory infiltrate of lymphocytes and plasma cells.

## Non-healing ulcer on the chest wall; scraping cytology shows epithelioid cells and Langhans-type giant cells — Most probable diagnosis?

The most probable diagnosis is **tuberculosis**. Epithelioid cells and Langhans giant cells [giant cells with peripheral horseshoe-shaped nuclei] indicate granulomatous inflammation, and tuberculosis is the prototype of a granulomatous disease caused by infection.

- Epithelioid cells are activated macrophages with pink granular cytoplasm, and Langhans giant cells are multinucleate cells formed by the fusion of activated macrophages.
- Tuberculosis should always be excluded as the cause when granulomas are identified. In this disease the granulomas are called **tubercles** and have central caseous necrosis.
- The diagnosis is confirmed by acid-fast stain for tubercle bacilli, culture and PCR.
- Other diseases with granulomatous inflammation are leprosy, syphilis, cat-scratch disease, sarcoidosis and Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease].

## 50-year-old woman with gum lesion (fibroblast proliferation and angiogenesis), long history of betel nut chewing — What type of inflammation occurs? What might be the complication?

**Chronic inflammation** occurs in this lesion. The complication to be feared is squamous cell carcinoma of the oral cavity.

**Type of inflammation**

- Chronic inflammation is a prolonged host response to a persistent stimulus. It is characterised by coexisting inflammation, tissue injury and attempted repair by scarring.
- Proliferation of fibroblasts with angiogenesis is the picture of granulation tissue, the attempted repair.
- The persistent stimulus here is the long history of chewing betel quid.

**Complication**

- Betel quid and paan contain areca nut, slaked lime and tobacco, and many of the ingredients can give rise to carcinogens.
- Chewing betel quid is a major regional predisposing influence for **squamous cell carcinoma** of the oral cavity.
- Persistent chronic inflammation also causes fibrosis (scarring).

## Raised/fleshy scar or mass at a surgical incision site, recurring after excision — Most probable diagnosis?

The most probable diagnosis is a **keloid**. A keloid is a raised scar that grows beyond the boundaries of the original wound and does not regress.

- Excessive formation of the components of the repair process can give rise to hypertrophic scars and keloids. Excessive collagen accumulates in a raised scar.
- **Hypertrophic scars** grow rapidly and contain abundant myofibroblasts, but they stay within the boundaries of the wound and tend to regress over several months.
- **Keloids** grow beyond the boundaries of the original wound and do not regress. Microscopically, thick connective tissue is deposited in the dermis.
- Keloid formation seems to be an individual predisposition.
- Rarely, incisional scars are followed by exuberant proliferation of fibroblasts that recurs after excision. This may be a desmoid (aggressive fibromatosis), a benign neoplasm of connective tissue.

## A 15y girl has pain, redness, swelling of forearm after trauma. What is the likely diagnosis?

The likely diagnosis is **acute inflammation** of the forearm.

- Pain (dolor), redness (rubor), swelling (tumour), warmth (calor) and loss of function (functio laesa) are the cardinal signs of acute inflammation.
- Trauma damages tissue, and necrotic cells, whatever the cause of cell death, elicit the production of inflammatory mediators.
- Redness and warmth are caused by increased blood flow, and swelling by oedema. Pain is due to the liberation of prostaglandins, neuropeptides and cytokines.

## Same girl: why does the swelling occur?

- **Oedema** denotes an excess of fluid in the interstitial tissue or serous cavities. It can be either an exudate or a transudate.
- Vasodilation is quickly followed by increased permeability of the microvasculature, by contraction of endothelial cells (histamine, bradykinin, leukotrienes) and by endothelial injury.
- Protein-rich fluid escapes from the vessels into the extravascular tissue. This escape of fluid, proteins and blood cells is called **exudation**.
- An **exudate** is an extravascular fluid with a high protein concentration that may contain cellular debris or, occasionally, some blood cells. Its presence implies an inflammatory process that has increased the permeability of small blood vessels.
- In contrast, a **transudate** has a low protein content, mostly albumin, and little or no cellular material. It is an ultrafiltrate of plasma produced by osmotic or hydrostatic imbalance across the vessel wall, without an increase in vascular permeability.
- Lymph flow is increased and helps to drain the oedema fluid.

## A 9y girl has a red, painful arm swelling with raised local temperature. What is the likely diagnosis?

The likely diagnosis is **acute inflammation** of the arm.

- Redness (rubor), pain (dolor), swelling (tumour) and raised local temperature (calor) are the cardinal signs of acute inflammation.
- Redness and warmth are caused by increased blood flow (vasodilation), and swelling by oedema from increased vascular permeability. Pain is due to prostaglandins, neuropeptides and cytokines.
- If the cause is infection with pyogenic bacteria such as staphylococci, the inflammation is purulent, and a localised collection of pus is an abscess.

## A 10y boy has pain and swelling in the right inguinal region for 7 days. What is the likely diagnosis?

The likely diagnosis is **acute inflammation of the inguinal lymph nodes** (reactive or inflammatory lymphadenitis).

- Lymphatic vessels and lymph nodes filter the extravascular fluid and clear microbes, so they take part in acute inflammation.
- The inflamed nodes are enlarged and painful because of hyperplasia of the lymphoid follicles and increased numbers of lymphocytes and macrophages.
- Red streaks near a skin wound represent inflamed lymphatic channels (lymphangitis) and are a telltale sign of bacterial infection in the area drained.

## A 7y boy has sudden red painful thigh swelling with raised local temperature. What is your probable diagnosis?

The probable diagnosis is **acute inflammation** of the thigh, most probably acute purulent (suppurative) inflammation with an abscess.

- Redness, pain, swelling and raised local temperature are the cardinal signs of acute inflammation.
- Purulent inflammation is most often caused by pyogenic bacteria such as staphylococci, which cause liquefactive necrosis.
- An **abscess** is a localised collection of pus, an exudate of neutrophils, liquefied debris of necrotic cells and oedema fluid.

## A 10y boy has fever, local swelling and pain. What is your provisional diagnosis?

The provisional diagnosis is **acute inflammation**, most probably acute purulent (suppurative) inflammation with an abscess.

- Local swelling and pain are signs of acute inflammation.
- Fever is a prominent systemic manifestation of acute inflammation, especially when it is associated with infection. It is caused by the cytokines IL-1 and TNF, which increase prostaglandin (PGE2) synthesis in the hypothalamus.
- Purulent inflammation is caused by pyogenic (pus-producing) bacteria such as staphylococci.

## How will you confirm this diagnosis (10y boy, fever, local swelling)?

1. **Total and differential leucocyte count.** Neutrophilic leucocytosis, usually 15,000 to 20,000 per microlitre, with a shift to the left (increased immature neutrophils in the blood).
2. **Acute-phase proteins.** Raised C-reactive protein, and a raised erythrocyte sedimentation rate (caused by fibrinogen).
3. **Examination of the pus** (aspirate or smear) for neutrophils, necrotic debris and microbes, and culture to identify the organism.

## Define edema.

**Oedema** is an excess of fluid in the interstitial tissue or serous cavities. It results from the movement of fluid from the vasculature into the interstitial spaces.

- The fluid may be protein poor (a transudate) or protein rich (an exudate).
- If a serosal surface is involved, fluid may accumulate within the adjacent body cavity as an **effusion**.

## Important causes of edema formation.

| Cause | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Increased hydrostatic pressure** | Impaired venous return: congestive heart failure, constrictive pericarditis, ascites (liver cirrhosis), venous thrombosis, external pressure (for example a mass), lower extremity inactivity with prolonged dependency. Arteriolar dilation: heat, neurohumoral dysregulation |
| **Reduced plasma osmotic pressure** (hypoproteinaemia) | Nephrotic syndrome (albumin lost in the urine), liver cirrhosis and protein malnutrition (reduced albumin synthesis), protein-losing gastroenteropathy |
| **Increased vascular permeability** | Acute and chronic inflammation, angiogenesis |
| **Lymphatic obstruction** | Trauma, fibrosis, invasive tumours, infectious agents (filariasis), surgery and irradiation (for example breast cancer treatment) |
| **Sodium and water retention** | Excessive salt intake with renal insufficiency, increased tubular reabsorption of sodium, renal hypoperfusion, increased renin-angiotensin-aldosterone secretion |

## What is anasarca?

**Anasarca** is generalised massive oedema.

- It occurs when oedema is widespread, as when reduced plasma oncotic pressure and increased hydrostatic pressure act together.
- Causes include right-sided heart failure, kwashiorkor (hypoalbuminaemia and generalised oedema, with ascites and puffiness of the face, hands and legs) and severe haemolytic anaemia with cardiac failure.

## What is effusion?

An **effusion** is an accumulation of fluid within a body cavity, when oedema involves a serosal surface.

- Effusions in the pleural cavity are called **hydrothorax**, in the pericardial cavity **hydropericardium**, and in the peritoneal cavity **hydroperitoneum** or **ascites**.
- Transudative effusions are typically protein poor, translucent and straw coloured. An exception is a chylous effusion from lymphatic blockage, which may be milky because of lipids absorbed from the gut.
- Exudative effusions are protein rich and often cloudy because of white cells.
- Effusions may be inflammatory or noninflammatory.

## Diseases where edema occurs due to increased hydrostatic pressure.

Increases in hydrostatic pressure are mainly caused by disorders that impair venous return.

- **Localised impairment:** deep venous thrombosis of a lower extremity, with oedema confined to the affected part. External pressure, such as a mass, also causes localised oedema.
- **Systemic increase in venous pressure** (widespread oedema): congestive heart failure, constrictive pericarditis and ascites in liver cirrhosis.
- Lower extremity inactivity with prolonged dependency.
- Arteriolar dilation (heat, neurohumoral dysregulation).

## State the pathophysiologic categories / classify edema.

| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Increased hydrostatic pressure: impaired venous return | Congestive heart failure; constrictive pericarditis; ascites (liver cirrhosis); venous obstruction or compression (thrombosis, external pressure such as a mass); lower extremity inactivity with prolonged dependency |
| **Increased hydrostatic pressure: arteriolar dilation** | Heat; neurohumoral dysregulation |
| **Reduced plasma osmotic pressure (hypoproteinaemia)** | Protein-losing glomerulopathies (nephrotic syndrome); liver cirrhosis; malnutrition; protein-losing gastroenteropathy |
| **Lymphatic obstruction** | Inflammatory; neoplastic; postsurgical; postirradiation |
| **Sodium retention** | Excessive salt intake with renal insufficiency; increased tubular reabsorption of sodium; renal hypoperfusion; increased renin-angiotensin-aldosterone secretion |
| **Inflammation** | Acute inflammation; chronic inflammation; angiogenesis |

## Give in brief the mechanisms of edema formation.

Under normal circumstances the tendency of vascular hydrostatic pressure to push water and salts out of capillaries is nearly balanced by the tendency of plasma colloid osmotic pressure to pull water and salts back into vessels. Elevated hydrostatic pressure or diminished colloid osmotic pressure disrupts this balance and increases the movement of fluid out of vessels. If the net rate of fluid influx exceeds the rate of lymphatic drainage, fluid accumulates.

1. **Increased hydrostatic pressure**, mainly caused by disorders that impair venous return, for example heart failure.
2. **Decreased colloid osmotic pressure** caused by reduced plasma albumin, either due to decreased synthesis (liver disease, protein malnutrition) or to increased loss (nephrotic syndrome).
3. **Increased vascular permeability**, for example in inflammation.
4. **Lymphatic obstruction**, for example by infection or neoplasia.
5. **Sodium and water retention**, for example in renal failure.

## Explain the mechanism of edema in nephrotic syndrome.

1. Increased permeability of the glomerular capillary wall allows proteins to escape from the plasma into the urinary space, resulting in **proteinuria**.
2. Heavy proteinuria depletes serum albumin at a rate beyond the compensatory synthetic capacity of the liver, resulting in **hypoalbuminaemia**. Increased renal catabolism of filtered albumin also contributes.
3. The generalised oedema is a direct consequence of decreased intravascular **colloid osmotic pressure** because of low plasma albumin.
4. Reduced plasma osmotic pressure leads to reduced intravascular volume, renal hypoperfusion and secondary hyperaldosteronism.
5. Sodium and water retention aggravates the oedema, because the primary defect, the low plasma protein level, persists. It seems to be due to several factors: compensatory secretion of aldosterone, mediated by the hypovolaemia-enhanced renin secretion; stimulation of the sympathetic nervous system; and a reduction in the secretion of natriuretic factors such as atrial natriuretic peptides.

The oedema is characteristically soft and pitting, and is most marked in the periorbital regions and dependent portions of the body. If severe, hypoalbuminaemia may also lead to pleural effusions and ascites.

## Write the mechanism of edema in nephritic syndrome.

1. The lesions that cause the nephritic syndrome have in common proliferation of the cells within the glomeruli, often accompanied by infiltration of inflammatory leucocytes.
2. This inflammatory reaction severely injures the capillary walls and induces haemodynamic changes that lead to a reduction in GFR.
3. The reduced GFR is manifested clinically by oliguria, **fluid retention** and azotaemia.
4. Salt retention, with obligate retention of associated water, causes both increased hydrostatic pressure (due to intravascular fluid volume expansion) and diminished vascular colloid osmotic pressure (due to dilution).
5. Oedema resulting from renal dysfunction often appears initially in parts of the body containing loose connective tissue, such as the eyelids; periorbital oedema is thus a characteristic finding in severe renal disease.

## How does edema due to cardiac failure develop?

1. Congestive heart failure is a cause of **impaired venous return**, which increases hydrostatic pressure.
2. One of the most important causes of renal hypoperfusion is congestive heart failure, which results in activation of the **renin-angiotensin-aldosterone axis**.
3. In early heart failure this response is beneficial: retention of sodium and water and other adaptations, including increased vascular tone and elevated levels of antidiuretic hormone, improve cardiac output and restore normal renal perfusion.
4. As heart failure worsens and cardiac output diminishes, the retained fluid merely increases the hydrostatic pressure, leading to oedema and effusions.

## Important diseases where transudates occur.

A **transudate** is a fluid with low protein content and little or no cellular material. It is formed by an osmotic or hydrostatic imbalance across the vessel wall, without increased vascular permeability.

- **Increased hydrostatic pressure:** congestive heart failure, constrictive pericarditis, ascites in liver cirrhosis and venous obstruction.
- **Decreased colloid osmotic pressure** from reduced plasma albumin, either by decreased synthesis (liver disease, protein malnutrition) or increased loss (nephrotic syndrome).
- **Sodium and water retention**, for example in renal failure.

## How are exudates and transudates formed?

Fluid movement across the capillary wall depends on the balance between hydrostatic and osmotic pressures. At the arterial end of a capillary bed the hydrostatic pressure is about 32 mm Hg, and at the venous end about 12 mm Hg. The mean colloid osmotic pressure of tissues is about 25 mm Hg, so the net flow of fluid is almost nil.

- An **exudate** is formed in inflammation because vascular permeability increases, with increased interendothelial spaces (contraction of endothelial cells, or endothelial injury). Protein-rich fluid escapes from the vessels.
- A **transudate** is formed when fluid leaks out because of increased hydrostatic pressure or decreased colloid osmotic pressure, with no increase in vascular permeability.

## Define exudate and transudate.

- An **exudate** is an extravascular fluid that has a high protein concentration and may contain cellular debris or, occasionally, some blood cells. Its presence implies the existence of an inflammatory process that has increased the permeability of small blood vessels.
- A **transudate** is a fluid with low protein content (most of which is albumin) and little or no cellular material. It is essentially an ultrafiltrate of blood plasma, produced as a result of osmotic or hydrostatic imbalance across the vessel wall without an increase in vascular permeability.
- **Pus** is a purulent exudate, rich in leucocytes (mostly neutrophils), the debris of dead cells and, in many cases, microbes.

## Differences between exudate and transudate.

| Feature | Exudate | Transudate |
|---|---|---|
| **Protein content** | High | Low (mostly albumin) |
| **Cells** | May contain cellular debris, occasionally blood cells. Effusions are often cloudy from white cells | Little or no cellular material |
| **Mechanism** | Increased permeability of small blood vessels (inflammation) | Osmotic or hydrostatic imbalance, with no increase in vascular permeability |
| **Appearance (effusions)** | Cloudy | Translucent and straw coloured |
| **Examples** | Inflammation, for example acute inflammation and pus | Heart failure, nephrotic syndrome, liver disease, malnutrition |

## Name the body fluids commonly examined in the laboratory.

Commonly examined body fluids:

- **Serous cavity fluids**: pleural, pericardial and peritoneal (ascitic) fluid, collected from effusions.
- **Blister fluid** and other serous exudates from inflammation.
- **Pus**, a purulent exudate.

## Tests performed for examination of body fluid.

An exudate and a transudate are recognised by these features:

- **Protein content:** high in an exudate, low (mostly albumin) in a transudate.
- **Cells:** an exudate contains cellular debris and white cells, and a transudate has little or no cellular material.
- **Appearance:** transudative effusions are translucent and straw coloured, and exudative effusions are cloudy. A chylous effusion is milky because of lipids.

## What is hyperemia?

**Hyperaemia** is an active process in which arteriolar dilation leads to increased blood flow.

- It occurs, for example, at sites of inflammation and in skeletal muscle during exercise.
- The affected tissues turn red (**erythema**) because of the increased delivery of oxygenated blood.
- Both hyperaemia and congestion stem from increased blood volumes within tissues.

## What is congestion?

**Congestion** is a passive process resulting from reduced venous outflow of blood from a tissue.

- It can be systemic, as in cardiac failure, or localised, as in isolated venous obstruction.
- Congested tissues have an abnormal blue-red colour (**cyanosis**) from the accumulation of deoxygenated haemoglobin.
- In long-standing chronic passive congestion, chronic hypoxia may cause ischaemic tissue injury and scarring. Capillary rupture can produce small haemorrhagic foci, with haemosiderin-laden macrophages.
- Increased hydrostatic pressure from congestion commonly leads to oedema.

## Differences between hyperemia and congestion.

| Feature | Hyperaemia | Congestion |
|---|---|---|
| Process | **Active** | **Passive** |
| Mechanism | Arteriolar dilation, with increased blood flow | Reduced venous outflow of blood from the tissue |
| Colour | Red (erythema), from oxygenated blood | Blue-red (cyanosis), from deoxygenated haemoglobin |
| Examples | Inflammation, skeletal muscle during exercise | Cardiac failure (systemic), isolated venous obstruction (localised) |
| Consequences | Increased blood flow | Oedema, and in chronic cases ischaemic injury, scarring and small haemorrhages |

## Common examples of each.

| | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Hyperaemia** | Sites of inflammation. Skeletal muscle during exercise |
| **Congestion** | Systemic: cardiac failure (for example chronic pulmonary congestion with heart failure cells, and chronic passive hepatic congestion with nutmeg liver). Localised: isolated venous obstruction |

## Describe briefly the role of platelets in normal haemostasis.

Platelets form the **primary plug** that initially seals vascular defects and provide a surface that binds and concentrates activated coagulation factors.

1. **Adhesion.** Platelets adhere to subendothelial von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII] (vWF) through **GpIb**; under high shear stress vWF binds both platelets and collagen, acting like a glue between them. Platelets also adhere to exposed collagen through GpIa/IIa and GpVI.
2. **Shape change.** Platelets change from smooth disks to spiky "sea urchins" with greatly increased surface area. GpIIb/IIIa undergoes a conformational change that increases its affinity for fibrinogen, and negatively charged phospholipids (particularly phosphatidylserine) move to the platelet surface, where they bind calcium and serve as nucleation sites for the assembly of coagulation factor complexes.
3. **Secretion (release reaction)** of granule contents, together with the shape change, is called platelet activation; it is triggered by thrombin and ADP.

- **Alpha granules** contain fibrinogen, factors V and XI, vWF, fibronectin, platelet factor 4, PDGF and TGF-β, and carry P-selectin on their membranes.
- **Dense granules** contain ADP, ATP, ionised calcium, serotonin and epinephrine.

4. **Recruitment.** ADP release begets additional rounds of platelet activation. Activated platelets also produce thromboxane A₂, a potent inducer of platelet aggregation.
5. **Aggregation.** Fibrinogen forms bridges between GpIIb/IIIa receptors on adjacent platelets. The initial wave of aggregation is reversible.
6. **Stabilisation.** Thrombin causes further platelet activation and aggregation, promotes irreversible platelet contraction, and converts fibrinogen into insoluble fibrin, which cements the platelets in place and creates the definitive secondary haemostatic plug.

## What are the antithrombotic properties of endothelial cells?

**Platelet inhibitory effects**

- Intact endothelium is a barrier that shields platelets from subendothelial vWF and collagen.
- It releases **prostacyclin (PGI₂)**, **nitric oxide (NO)** and **adenosine diphosphatase**, which degrades ADP.
- PGI₂ is produced by COX-1 and NO is the product of endothelial nitric oxide synthase (eNOS); their production appears to be regulated by blood flow.

**Anticoagulant effects**

- Normal endothelium shields coagulation factors from tissue factor in vessel walls.
- **Thrombomodulin** and **endothelial protein C receptor** bind thrombin and protein C. In this complex, thrombin loses its ability to activate coagulation factors and platelets and instead activates protein C, which with its cofactor protein S inhibits coagulation cofactors Va and VIIIa.
- **Heparin-like molecules** on the endothelial surface bind and activate antithrombin III, which inhibits thrombin and factors IXa, Xa, XIa and XIIa.
- **Tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI)** requires protein S as a cofactor and binds and inhibits tissue factor/factor VIIa complexes.

**Fibrinolytic effects**

- Normal endothelial cells synthesise **tissue plasminogen activator (t-PA)**, a key component of the fibrinolytic pathway.

## Define thrombus.

A **thrombus** is a blood clot that forms within an intact blood vessel or within the chambers of the heart.

- **Haemostasis** is the process by which blood clots form at sites of vascular injury. In thrombotic disorders, clots (thrombi) form within intact blood vessels or the heart chambers.
- The primary abnormalities that lead to thrombosis are endothelial injury, stasis or turbulent blood flow, and hypercoagulability of the blood (the Virchow triad [stasis, endothelial injury and hypercoagulability]).

## Virchow's triad.

1. **Endothelial injury** (for example by toxins, hypertension, inflammation or metabolic products), associated with endothelial activation and changes in endothelial gene expression that favour coagulation.
2. **Abnormal blood flow: stasis or turbulence** (for example due to aneurysms or atherosclerotic plaque).
3. **Hypercoagulability**, either primary or secondary.

- Primary: factor V Leiden (a mutation named after the city in the Netherlands where it was discovered), increased prothrombin synthesis, antithrombin deficiency.
- Secondary: bed rest, tissue damage, malignancy, antiphospholipid antibodies (antiphospholipid antibody syndrome) or antibodies against PF4/heparin complexes (heparin-induced thrombocytopenia).

Endothelial integrity is the most important factor. Injury to endothelial cells can alter local blood flow and affect coagulability. Abnormal blood flow, in turn, can cause endothelial injury. These factors may promote thrombosis independently or in combination.

## Why does prolonged bed rest result in pulmonary embolism?

1. Bed rest and immobilisation reduce the milking action of the leg muscles, which results in **stasis** in the leg veins.
2. Stasis is a major contributor to the development of venous thrombi. It promotes endothelial activation, brings platelets into contact with the endothelium, and prevents washout and dilution of activated clotting factors by fresh flowing blood and the inflow of clotting factor inhibitors.
3. **Deep vein thrombosis** of the lower extremity develops; veins of the lower extremities are involved in 90% of venous thrombi.
4. The propagating portion of the thrombus is poorly attached and fragments. The fragments are carried through progressively larger veins and the right side of the heart into the pulmonary arterial vasculature as a **pulmonary embolus**.
5. In 70% to 80% of cases, pulmonary embolism originates from leg deep vein thrombosis, so the risk factors for pulmonary embolism are the same as for deep vein thrombosis.

## What are mural thrombi?

Thrombi occurring in heart chambers or in the aortic lumen are designated **mural thrombi**.

## Differences between thrombus and post-mortem clot.

| Feature | Thrombus | Postmortem clot |
|---|---|---|
| **Time of formation** | Formed in flowing blood during life (antemortem) | Forms after death |
| Lines of Zahn [alternating pale platelet-fibrin and dark red cell layers in a thrombus] (alternating pale platelet and fibrin deposits and darker red cell-rich layers) | Present; they signify that the thrombus formed in flowing blood | Absent; bland and nonlaminated |
| **Consistency** | Firm | Gelatinous |
| **Appearance** | Laminated | Dark-red dependent portion where red cells have settled by gravity and a yellow "chicken fat" upper portion |
| **Attachment to vessel wall** | Focally attached to the underlying vascular surface | Usually not attached to the underlying vessel wall |

## What is thrombosis?

**Thrombosis** is inappropriate clotting: blood clots (thrombi) form within intact blood vessels or within the chambers of the heart.

## Discuss the role of endothelial injury in thrombus formation.

- Endothelial integrity is the most important factor in Virchow's triad [stasis, endothelial injury and hypercoagulability]. Injury to endothelial cells can alter local blood flow and affect coagulability, and abnormal blood flow can in turn cause endothelial injury.
- Severe endothelial injury may trigger thrombosis by exposing **vWF and tissue factor**.
- Inflammation and other noxious stimuli also promote thrombosis by shifting the pattern of gene expression in endothelium to one that is prothrombotic, called **endothelial activation or dysfunction**. It can be produced by physical injury, infectious agents, abnormal blood flow, inflammatory mediators, metabolic abnormalities such as hypercholesterolaemia or homocystinaemia, and toxins absorbed from cigarette smoke. It is believed to have an important role in triggering arterial thrombotic events.

**Prothrombotic alterations**

- **Procoagulant changes:** endothelial cells activated by inflammatory cytokines downregulate thrombomodulin, enhancing the procoagulant and proinflammatory actions of thrombin. Inflamed endothelium also downregulates protein C and tissue factor pathway inhibitor.
- **Antifibrinolytic effects:** activated endothelial cells secrete plasminogen activator inhibitors (PAIs), which limit fibrinolysis, and downregulate the expression of t-PA.

Cardiac and arterial clots are typically rich in platelets, and platelet adherence and activation is believed to be a necessary prerequisite for thrombus formation under high shear stress, such as exists in arteries.

## How does altered blood flow cause thrombosis?

Turbulence contributes to arterial and cardiac thrombosis by causing endothelial injury or dysfunction and by forming countercurrents that contribute to local pockets of stasis. Stasis is a major contributor to the development of venous thrombi.

Normal blood flow is laminar, so the platelets flow centrally in the vessel lumen, separated from the endothelium by a slower moving layer of plasma. Turbulence and stasis:

- Promote endothelial activation, enhancing procoagulant activity and leucocyte adhesion
- Disrupt laminar flow and bring platelets into contact with the endothelium
- Prevent washout and dilution of activated clotting factors by fresh flowing blood and the inflow of clotting factor inhibitors

**Clinical settings**

- Ulcerated atherosclerotic plaques expose subendothelial vWF and tissue factor and also cause turbulence.
- Aneurysms result in local stasis and are fertile sites for thrombosis.
- Acute myocardial infarction results in areas of noncontractile myocardium and sometimes in cardiac aneurysm; both are associated with stasis and flow abnormalities that promote cardiac mural thrombi.
- Rheumatic mitral valve stenosis results in left atrial dilation; in conjunction with atrial fibrillation, a dilated atrium is a site of profound stasis and a prime location for thrombosis.
- Hyperviscosity (as in polycythaemia vera) increases resistance to flow and causes small vessel stasis.
- The deformed red cells in sickle cell anaemia impede blood flow through small vessels, and the resulting stasis predisposes to thrombosis.

## Write down the pathogenesis of venous thrombus.

1. Venous thrombi characteristically occur at sites of **stasis**, which is a major contributor to their development. Bed rest and immobilisation reduce the milking action of the leg muscles, resulting in stasis, and congestive heart failure is also a cause of impaired venous return.
2. Stasis prevents washout and dilution of activated clotting factors by fresh flowing blood and the inflow of clotting factor inhibitors, and it promotes endothelial activation.
3. **Hypercoagulability** has a particularly important role in venous thrombosis. It may be primary (genetic), such as factor V Leiden and the prothrombin G20210A mutation, or secondary (acquired).
4. The thrombus forms in the sluggish venous circulation, so it contains more enmeshed red cells and relatively few platelets (a **red thrombus** or **stasis thrombus**). It is firm, focally attached to the vessel wall, contains lines of Zahn [alternating pale platelet-fibrin and dark red cell layers in a thrombus], and is almost invariably occlusive, forming a long luminal cast.
5. Venous thrombi extend in the direction of blood flow, towards the heart. The propagating portion is often poorly attached and therefore prone to fragmentation and embolisation.

**Predisposing factors in the lower extremity (90% of cases)**

- Bed rest and immobilisation, and congestive heart failure.
- Trauma, surgery and burns, which not only immobilise a person but are also associated with vascular insults, procoagulant release from injured tissues, increased hepatic synthesis of coagulation factors and decreased t-PA production.
- Pregnancy, through decreased venous return from leg veins and systemic hypercoagulability associated with the hormonal changes of late pregnancy and the postpartum period.
- Disseminated cancers, through tumour-associated inflammation and release of coagulation factors (for example tissue factor) and procoagulants (for example mucin); this is called migratory thrombophlebitis or Trousseau syndrome [migratory thrombophlebitis associated with cancer].
- Advanced age.

## What is Virchow's triad of thrombogenesis?

The primary abnormalities that lead to thrombosis are (1) **endothelial injury**, (2) **stasis or turbulent blood flow** and (3) **hypercoagulability** of the blood, the so-called **Virchow triad** [stasis, endothelial injury and hypercoagulability].

## Mention the causes of hypercoagulability of blood.

**Primary (genetic)**

- Common: factor V Leiden (named after the city in the Netherlands where it was discovered; arginine to glutamine substitution at amino acid residue 506, leading to resistance to activated protein C); prothrombin mutation (G20210A noncoding sequence variant leading to increased prothrombin levels); increased levels of factors VIII, IX, XI or fibrinogen
- Rare: antithrombin III deficiency; protein C deficiency; protein S deficiency
- Very rare: fibrinolysis defects; homozygous homocystinuria (deficiency of cystathionine β-synthetase)

**Secondary (acquired)**

- Strong risk factors: prolonged bed rest or immobilisation; myocardial infarction; atrial fibrillation; tissue injury (surgery, fracture, burn); cancer; prosthetic cardiac valves; disseminated intravascular coagulation; heparin-induced thrombocytopenia; antiphospholipid antibody syndrome
- Other risk factors: cardiomyopathy; nephrotic syndrome; hyperoestrogenic states (pregnancy and postpartum); oral contraceptive use; sickle cell anaemia; smoking

## Give the fate of a thrombus.

If a patient survives the initial thrombosis, in the ensuing days to weeks thrombi undergo some combination of four events:

1. **Propagation.** Thrombi accumulate additional platelets and fibrin and enlarge.
2. **Embolisation.** Thrombi dislodge and travel to other sites in the vasculature.
3. **Dissolution.** The result of fibrinolysis, which can lead to the rapid shrinkage and total disappearance of recent thrombi. Extensive fibrin deposition and cross-linking in older thrombi render them more resistant to lysis, so fibrinolytic agents such as t-PA are generally effective only when given during the first few hours of a thrombotic event.
4. **Organisation and recanalisation.** Older thrombi become organised by the ingrowth of endothelial cells, smooth muscle cells and fibroblasts. Capillary channels eventually form that reestablish the continuity of the original lumen, albeit to a variable degree. With remodelling and contraction of the mesenchymal elements, only a fibrous lump may remain to mark the original thrombus.

Occasionally the centres of thrombi undergo enzymatic digestion, presumably from the release of lysosomal enzymes from trapped leucocytes and platelets. In the setting of bacteraemia such thrombi may become infected, producing an inflammatory mass that erodes and weakens the vessel wall and may result in a mycotic aneurysm.

## Define embolus.

An **embolus** is a detached intravascular solid, liquid, or gaseous mass that is carried by the blood from its point of origin to a distant site, where it often obstructs blood flow and causes tissue dysfunction or infarction.

## Classify embolus.

**By composition**

- **Thromboembolus:** a dislodged thrombus; most emboli are of this type.
- **Fat embolus:** fat droplets, sometimes with haematopoietic bone marrow.
- **Air or nitrogen bubbles:** air embolism and decompression sickness.
- **Cholesterol embolus:** atherosclerotic debris.
- **Tumour fragments.**
- **Bone marrow.**
- **Foreign bodies.**
- **Amniotic fluid.**

**By the site of lodgement**

- **Pulmonary embolism:** originates from deep vein thrombosis.
- **Systemic embolism:** arterial emboli that travel to a wide variety of sites.
- **Paradoxical embolism:** a venous embolus that passes through an interatrial or interventricular defect and gains access to the systemic arterial circulation.

## Examples of each type of embolus.

| Type of embolus | Example |
|---|---|
| **Thromboembolus** | Embolus from a lower extremity deep venous thrombosis lodged in the pulmonary artery; emboli from intracardiac mural thrombi, aortic aneurysms, atherosclerotic plaques or valvular vegetations in the systemic circulation |
| **Fat and bone marrow embolus** | Fractures of long bones; rarely soft tissue trauma and burns |
| **Air embolus** | Neurosurgery in the "sitting position"; endovascular and interventional procedures; mechanical ventilation; obstetric or laparoscopic procedures; chest wall injury |
| **Nitrogen bubbles** | Decompression sickness in scuba and deep-sea divers, underwater construction workers, and persons in unpressurised aircraft who undergo rapid ascent |
| **Amniotic fluid embolus** | Labour and the immediate postpartum period, through a tear in the placental membranes or rupture of uterine veins |
| Atherosclerotic debris (cholesterol emboli), tumour fragments, foreign bodies | Named as rare emboli |

## Common sources of emboli.

- **Deep vein thrombosis of the leg:** the source of 70% to 80% of pulmonary emboli.
- **Intracardiac mural thrombi:** the source of 80% of systemic emboli. Two-thirds are associated with left ventricular wall infarcts and another one-fourth with left atrial dilation and fibrillation.

## Other sources of emboli.

The remainder of systemic **emboli** originate from:

- **Aortic aneurysms**
- **Atherosclerotic plaques**
- **Valvular vegetations**
- **Venous thrombi** (paradoxical emboli)

In **10%** to **15%** of systemic **emboli** the origin is unknown.

## How does fat embolism occur?

1. **Fat embolism typically** follows fractures of long bones or, rarely, soft tissue trauma and burns; it occurs in some 90% of individuals with severe skeletal injuries.
2. **The injury ruptures** vascular sinusoids or small venules in the marrow.
3. **Marrow and adipose** tissue herniate into the vascular space.
4. **Microscopic fat globules**, sometimes with associated haematopoietic bone marrow, travel to the lung.

## What is fat embolism syndrome?

**Fat embolism syndrome** is the term applied to the minority of patients with fat embolism who become symptomatic. It is characterised by pulmonary insufficiency, neurologic symptoms, anaemia and thrombocytopenia, and is fatal in 5% to 15% of cases.

## What is decompression sickness / caisson disease?

**Decompression sickness** is a particular form of gas embolism that occurs when individuals experience sudden decreases in atmospheric pressure.

**Caisson disease** is a more chronic form of decompression sickness (named for the pressurised vessels used in bridge construction; workers in these vessels suffered both acute and chronic forms). In caisson disease, persistence of gas emboli in the skeletal system leads to multiple foci of ischaemic necrosis.

## How much air is needed to produce a clinical effect?

- A large volume of air, generally more than **100 mL**, is necessary to produce a clinical effect in the pulmonary circulation.
- Introduction of **300 to 500 mL** of air over several seconds may be fatal.

## Clinical effects of caisson disease.

**Chronic form (caisson disease)**

- Persistence of gas emboli in the skeletal system leads to multiple foci of **ischaemic necrosis**.
- The more common sites are the **femoral heads, tibia and humeri**.

**Acute decompression sickness, also suffered by caisson workers**

- **The bends:** rapid formation of gas bubbles within skeletal muscles and supporting tissues in and about joints causes a painful condition. It was so named in the 1880s because those afflicted characteristically arched their backs in a manner reminiscent of the Grecian bend (a then-popular women's fashion pose).
- **The chokes:** gas bubbles in the pulmonary vasculature cause oedema, haemorrhage, and focal atelectasis or emphysema, leading to a form of respiratory distress.
- Even small intravascular bubbles may have physical effects, caused by inflammatory and thrombogenic host responses.

## Why is amniotic fluid embolism so dangerous?

- It is the fifth most common cause of maternal mortality worldwide and accounts for roughly 10% of maternal deaths in the United States.
- It is an ominous complication of labour and the immediate postpartum period; the incidence is only approximately 2 to 6 in 100,000 deliveries, but the mortality rate is up to 60%.
- Approximately half of those who survive have permanent neurologic dysfunction.
- The onset is characterised by sudden severe dyspnoea, cyanosis and shock, followed by neurologic impairment ranging from headache to seizures and coma, and by **DIC**.
- Amniotic fluid contains many procoagulants, such as activated coagulation factors II, VII, X and tissue factor.
- Much of the morbidity and mortality stems from activation of coagulation and components of the innate immune system and release of vasoactive substances, rather than mechanical obstruction of pulmonary vessels by amniotic debris.
- The vasoactive substances cause acute **pulmonary hypertension** and right-heart failure, leading to hypoxia, left-heart failure, pulmonary oedema and diffuse alveolar damage.

## Sources of pulmonary embolism.

- Pulmonary emboli originate from **deep vein thrombosis**.
- In 70% to 80% of cases, pulmonary embolism originates from **leg deep vein thrombosis**.
- Deep vein thrombosis of one of the large leg veins at or above the knee (the popliteal, femoral and iliac veins) is considered serious because such thrombi more often embolise to the lungs.

## Clinical consequences of pulmonary embolism.

- Most pulmonary emboli (60% to 80%) are clinically silent because they are small. With time they become organised and are incorporated into the vascular wall; in some cases, organisation of the thromboembolus leaves behind a delicate, bridging fibrous web.
- **Sudden death, acute right-heart failure (cor pulmonale) or cardiovascular collapse** occurs when emboli obstruct 60% or more of the pulmonary circulation.
- Embolic obstruction of **medium-sized arteries** with subsequent vascular rupture can result in pulmonary haemorrhage but usually does not cause pulmonary infarction. This is because the lung is supplied by both the pulmonary arteries and the bronchial arteries, and the intact bronchial circulation is usually sufficient to perfuse the affected area. If the bronchial arterial flow is compromised (for example by left-sided cardiac failure), infarction may occur.
- Embolic obstruction of **small end-arteriolar pulmonary branches** often does produce haemorrhage or infarction.
- Multiple emboli over time may cause **pulmonary hypertension and right ventricular failure**.

## Fate of pulmonary embolism.

- Most pulmonary emboli are small and clinically silent. With time they become **organised** and are incorporated into the vascular wall, sometimes leaving behind a delicate, bridging fibrous web.
- Emboli obstructing 60% or more of the pulmonary circulation cause sudden death, acute right-heart failure (cor pulmonale) or cardiovascular collapse. An estimated 20% of individuals with pulmonary embolism die before or shortly after a diagnosis is made.
- Frequently there are multiple emboli, occurring sequentially or simultaneously as a shower of smaller emboli from a single large mass. The patient who has had one pulmonary embolism is at high risk for more.
- Multiple emboli over time may cause pulmonary hypertension and right ventricular failure.

## Classify emboli on the basis of their composition.

- **Thromboembolus:** a dislodged thrombus; most emboli are of this type.
- **Fat droplets**, sometimes with haematopoietic bone marrow.
- **Air or nitrogen bubbles.**
- **Atherosclerotic debris** (cholesterol emboli).
- **Tumour fragments.**
- **Bone marrow.**
- **Foreign bodies.**
- **Amniotic fluid.**

## Write in short the pathogenesis/effects of systemic embolism.

**Sources of systemic emboli**

- Most systemic emboli (80%) arise from **intracardiac mural thrombi**, two-thirds of which are associated with left ventricular wall infarcts and another one-fourth with left atrial dilation and fibrillation.
- The remainder originate from **aortic aneurysms**, **atherosclerotic plaques**, **valvular vegetations** or **venous thrombi** (paradoxical emboli).
- A paradoxical embolus is a venous embolus that passes through an interatrial or interventricular defect and gains access to the systemic arterial circulation.
- 10% to 15% are of unknown origin.

**Sites of lodgement**

- In contrast to venous emboli, the vast majority of which lodge in the lung, arterial emboli can travel to a wide variety of sites.
- The point of arrest depends on the source and the relative amount of blood flow that downstream tissues receive.
- Most come to rest in the lower extremities (75%) or the brain (10%).
- Intestines, kidneys, spleen and upper extremities may also be involved.

**Effects**

- The consequences depend on the vulnerability of the affected tissue to ischaemia, the calibre of the occluded vessel, and whether a collateral blood supply exists.
- In general, the outcome is tissue **infarction**.


## Write a short note on embolism / emboli.

**Definition**

An **embolus** is a detached intravascular solid, liquid or gaseous mass that is carried by the blood from its point of origin to a distant site, where it often obstructs blood flow and causes tissue dysfunction or infarction.

**Composition**

- Most emboli are dislodged thrombi, hence the term **thromboembolism**.
- Other rare emboli are composed of fat droplets, air or nitrogen bubbles, atherosclerotic debris (cholesterol emboli), tumour fragments, bone marrow, or even foreign bodies.

**Pathogenesis**

- Emboli travel through the blood until they encounter vessels too small to permit further passage, causing partial or complete vascular occlusion.
- Depending on where they originate, emboli can lodge anywhere in the vascular tree.

**Types**

- **Pulmonary emboli** derive primarily from lower extremity deep vein thrombosis.
