- **Systemic emboli** derive from cardiac mural or valvular thrombi, aortic aneurysms or atherosclerotic plaques.
- **Fat embolism** occurs after crushing injuries to the bones.
- **Air embolism** occurs upon rapid decompression, most commonly in divers.
- **Amniotic fluid embolism** is a rare complication of childbirth.

**Clinical consequences**

- The clinical consequences vary widely, depending on the size and the position of the lodged embolus, as well as the vascular bed that is impacted.
- Pulmonary emboli may cause right-sided heart failure, pulmonary haemorrhage, pulmonary infarction or sudden death.
- Whether a systemic embolus causes tissue infarction depends on the site of embolisation and the presence or absence of collateral circulation.


## Write a short note on fat embolism.

**Definition**

**Fat embolism** refers to the presence of microscopic fat globules, sometimes with associated haematopoietic bone marrow, in the vasculature.

**Aetiology**

- It typically follows **fractures of long bones**; rarely it occurs in the setting of soft tissue trauma and burns.
- It is common, occurring in some 90% of individuals with severe skeletal injuries.

**Pathogenesis**

- The injuries presumably rupture vascular sinusoids or small venules in the marrow, allowing marrow and adipose tissue to herniate into the vascular space and travel to the lung.
- **Fat embolism syndrome** is the term applied to the minority of patients who become symptomatic.
- Its pathogenesis involves both **mechanical obstruction** and **biochemical injury**.
- Fat microemboli and associated red cell and platelet aggregates occlude the pulmonary and cerebral microvasculature.
- Release of free fatty acids from the fat globules causes local toxic injury to endothelium.
- Platelet activation and granulocyte recruitment, with release of free radicals, proteases and eicosanoids, complete the vascular assault.
- Thrombocytopenia is attributed to platelet adhesion to fat globules and subsequent aggregation or splenic sequestration.
- Anaemia can result from red cell aggregation or haemolysis.

**Morphology**

- Lipids are dissolved out of tissue preparations by the solvents used in paraffin embedding.
- Microscopic demonstration of fat microglobules, in the absence of accompanying marrow elements, therefore requires specialised techniques, including **frozen sections** and **stains for fat**.

**Clinical features and complications**

- Fat embolism syndrome is characterised by **pulmonary insufficiency**, **neurological symptoms**, **anaemia** and **thrombocytopenia**.
- Typically 1 to 3 days after injury there is sudden onset of tachypnoea, dyspnoea, tachycardia, irritability and restlessness, which can progress to delirium or coma.
- A diffuse **petechial rash** (seen in 20% to 50% of cases) is related to the rapid onset of thrombocytopenia and is a useful diagnostic clue.
- It is fatal in 5% to 15% of cases.


## Write a short note on air embolism.

**Definition**

Gas bubbles within the circulation can coalesce to form frothy masses that obstruct vascular flow and cause distal ischaemic injury.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Air embolism occurs when there is communication between the vasculature and outside air and a negative pressure gradient that "sucks" in the air.
- Air may be introduced into the cerebral circulation by neurosurgery in the "sitting position", creating a gravitational gradient.
- Air may also be introduced during endovascular and interventional procedures, during mechanical ventilation, during obstetric or laparoscopic procedures, or because of chest wall injury.
- A large volume of air, generally more than 100 mL, is necessary to produce a clinical effect in the pulmonary circulation.
- Introduction of 300 to 500 mL of air over several seconds may be fatal.

**Clinical features**

- Entry of air in the pulmonary vasculature blocks perfusion of the downstream region and induces an intense inflammatory response, with release of cytokines that may injure the alveoli.
- Bubbles in the central nervous system can cause memory loss, ataxia, visual disturbances and even sudden onset of coma.

**Decompression sickness**

- It is a particular form of gas embolism that occurs when individuals experience sudden decreases in atmospheric pressure; scuba and deep-sea divers, underwater construction workers and persons in unpressurised aircraft who undergo rapid ascent are at risk.
- On breathing air at high pressure, increased amounts of gas, particularly nitrogen, are dissolved in the blood and tissues; if the diver ascends too rapidly, the nitrogen comes out of solution in the tissues and the blood.
- The **bends** is the painful condition caused by rapid formation of gas bubbles within skeletal muscles and supporting tissues in and about joints.
- The **chokes** is a form of respiratory distress in which gas bubbles in the lung vasculature cause oedema, haemorrhage, and focal atelectasis or emphysema.
- **Caisson disease** is a more chronic form, in which persistence of gas emboli in the skeletal system leads to multiple foci of ischaemic necrosis; the more common sites are the femoral heads, tibia and humeri.
- Acute decompression sickness is treated by placing the patient in a chamber under sufficiently high pressure to force the gas bubbles back into solution; subsequent slow decompression permits gradual resorption and exhalation of the gases.


## Write a short note on pulmonary embolism.

**Definition and incidence**

- Pulmonary emboli originate from **deep vein thrombosis (DVT)** and are a common, serious form of thromboembolic disease.
- In 70% to 80% of cases, pulmonary embolism originates from leg DVT.
- Incidence is an estimated 60 to 120 cases per 100,000 people in the United States; it is somewhat more common in males than in females.
- It causes 60,000 to 100,000 deaths per year in the United States; an estimated 20% of individuals with pulmonary embolism die before or shortly after a diagnosis is made.

**Risk factors**

- The risk factors are the same as for DVT.
- Strong risk factors are prolonged bed rest or immobilisation, myocardial infarction, atrial fibrillation, tissue injury (surgery, fracture, burn), cancer, prosthetic cardiac valves, disseminated intravascular coagulation, heparin-induced thrombocytopenia and antiphospholipid antibody syndrome.

**Pathogenesis and morphology**

- Fragmented thrombi from DVT are carried through progressively larger veins and the right side of the heart before lodging in the pulmonary arterial vasculature.
- Depending on the size of the embolus, it can occlude the main pulmonary artery, straddle the pulmonary artery bifurcation (**saddle embolus**), or pass out into the smaller, branching arteries.
- Frequently there are multiple emboli, occurring either sequentially or simultaneously as a shower of smaller emboli from a single large mass; the patient who has had one pulmonary embolism is at high risk for more.
- Rarely, a venous embolus passes through an interatrial or interventricular defect and gains access to the systemic arterial circulation (**paradoxical embolism**).

**Clinical features and complications**

- Most pulmonary emboli (60% to 80%) are clinically **silent** because they are small; with time they become organised and are incorporated into the vascular wall, sometimes leaving a delicate, bridging fibrous web.
- **Sudden death**, acute right-heart failure (**cor pulmonale**), or cardiovascular collapse occurs when emboli obstruct 60% or more of the pulmonary circulation.
- Obstruction of medium-sized arteries with subsequent vascular rupture can result in **pulmonary haemorrhage** but usually does not cause pulmonary infarction, because the lung is supplied by both the pulmonary and the bronchial arteries.
- If the bronchial arterial flow is compromised (for example by left-sided cardiac failure), infarction may occur.
- Obstruction of small end-arteriolar pulmonary branches often produces haemorrhage or **infarction**.
- Multiple emboli over time may cause **pulmonary hypertension** and **right ventricular failure**.


## Male (30 or 38 years) hospitalised for femur fracture; after two weeks sudden chest pain, cough and haemoptysis — Most probable diagnosis? Cause of this complication?

**Most probable diagnosis**

- **Pulmonary embolism**, with pulmonary haemorrhage or infarction.
- Embolic obstruction of small end-arteriolar pulmonary branches often produces haemorrhage or infarction.
- Fat embolism syndrome is less likely, because it typically begins 1 to 3 days after injury.

**Cause of this complication**

- **Deep vein thrombosis** of the leg, from which fragments of thrombus detach and are carried through progressively larger veins and the right side of the heart into the pulmonary arterial vasculature. In 70% to 80% of cases, pulmonary embolism originates from leg deep vein thrombosis.
- Bed rest and immobilisation reduce the milking action of the leg muscles, resulting in stasis.
- Trauma, surgery and burns not only immobilise a person but are also associated with vascular insults, procoagulant release from injured tissues, increased hepatic synthesis of coagulation factors and decreased t-PA production.

## A 38y male with femur fracture develops chest pain, cough and haemoptysis after 2 weeks. What is the most likely diagnosis?

The most likely diagnosis is **pulmonary embolism**, from **deep vein thrombosis** of the leg.

- Tissue injury (fracture) and prolonged bed rest or immobilisation are strong risk factors for thrombosis.
- Pulmonary emboli originate from DVT, in 70% to 80% of cases from leg DVT.
- Embolic obstruction of small end-arteriolar pulmonary branches often produces **haemorrhage or infarction**.
- Fat embolism syndrome, in contrast, typically appears 1 to 3 days after injury with tachypnoea, dyspnoea, tachycardia, irritability and restlessness.


## Same patient: what is the cause of this complication?

The cause is a **deep vein thrombosis** of the leg whose fragments have embolised to the lung.

- Bed rest and immobilisation reduce the milking action of the leg muscles, resulting in **stasis**.
- Trauma and surgery not only immobilise the person but are also associated with **vascular insults**, **procoagulant release** from injured tissues, **increased hepatic synthesis** of coagulation factors and **decreased t-PA production**.
- Venous thrombi extend in the direction of blood flow, and the propagating portion is often poorly attached and therefore prone to fragmentation and embolisation.
- DVT in a large leg vein at or above the knee (popliteal, femoral, iliac) more often embolises to the lungs.
- DVT is asymptomatic in approximately 50% of affected individuals and is recognised only in retrospect after embolisation.


## What is an infarct?

An **infarct** is an area of ischaemic necrosis caused by occlusion of either the arterial supply or the venous drainage.

## Underlying causes of infarction.

**Arterial obstruction**

- **Arterial thrombosis or arterial embolism** underlies most infarctions.
- Less common causes: local vasospasm, haemorrhage into an atheromatous plaque, and extrinsic vessel compression (for example by tumour).

**Other uncommon causes**

- Torsion of a vessel (for example in testicular torsion or bowel volvulus).
- Traumatic vascular rupture.
- Vascular compromise by oedema (for example anterior compartment syndrome of the leg) or by entrapment in a hernia sac.

**Venous thrombosis**

- Venous thrombosis can cause infarction, but the more common outcome is just congestion, because bypass channels rapidly open and permit vascular outflow, which then improves arterial inflow.
- Infarcts caused by venous thrombosis are more likely in organs with a single efferent vein, such as the testis and ovary.

## Sites where white infarct occurs.

White infarcts occur with arterial occlusions in solid organs with end-arterial circulation, and **where** tissue density limits the seepage of blood from adjoining capillary beds into the necrotic area.

- **Heart**
- **Spleen**
- **Kidney**

## Factors that influence the development of an infarct.

1. **Anatomy of the vascular supply.** The availability of an alternative blood supply is the most important determinant of whether vessel occlusion will cause tissue damage.

- The lungs have a dual pulmonary and bronchial artery blood supply that protects against thromboembolism-induced infarction.
- The liver, with its dual hepatic artery and portal vein circulation, and the hand and forearm, with their dual radial and ulnar arterial supply, are relatively resistant to infarction.
- Renal and splenic circulations are end-arterial, and vascular obstruction generally causes tissue death.

2. **Rate of occlusion.** Slowly developing occlusions are less likely to cause infarction because they provide time for development of collateral pathways of perfusion. For example, small interarteriolar anastomoses interconnect the three major coronary arteries; if one coronary artery is occluded slowly, flow within this collateral circulation may increase sufficiently to prevent infarction.
3. **Tissue vulnerability to hypoxia.** Neurons undergo irreversible damage when deprived of their blood supply for only 3 to 4 minutes. Myocardial cells die after only 20 to 30 minutes of ischaemia. Fibroblasts within myocardium remain viable even after many hours of ischaemia.
4. **Hypoxaemia.** Abnormally low blood oxygen content, regardless of cause, increases both the likelihood and extent of infarction.

## Organs where red infarct occurs, and why.

Red (haemorrhagic) infarcts occur:

1. With **venous occlusions**, for example testicular torsion.
2. In **loose, spongy tissues**, for example the lung, where blood can collect in the infarcted zone.
3. In tissues with **dual circulations**, for example the lung and small intestine, which allow blood to flow from an unobstructed parallel supply into a necrotic zone.
4. In tissues **previously congested** by sluggish venous outflow.
5. When **flow is reestablished** to a site of previous arterial occlusion and necrosis, for example following angioplasty of an arterial obstruction.

In the lung, haemorrhagic infarcts are the rule.

## What are the factors that influence formation of infarct?

1. **Anatomy of the vascular supply.** The availability of an alternative blood supply is the most important determinant of whether vessel occlusion will cause tissue damage.

- The lungs have a dual pulmonary and bronchial artery blood supply that protects against thromboembolism-induced infarction.
- The liver, with its dual hepatic artery and portal vein circulation, and the hand and forearm, with their dual radial and ulnar arterial supply, are relatively resistant to infarction.
- Renal and splenic circulations are end-arterial, and vascular obstruction generally causes tissue death.

2. **Rate of occlusion.** Slowly developing occlusions are less likely to cause infarction because they provide time for development of collateral pathways of perfusion.

- Small interarteriolar anastomoses, normally with minimal functional flow, interconnect the three major coronary arteries of the heart.
- If one coronary artery is occluded slowly by an encroaching atherosclerotic plaque, flow within this collateral circulation may increase sufficiently to prevent infarction.

3. **Tissue vulnerability to hypoxia.**

- Neurons undergo irreversible damage when deprived of their blood supply for only 3 to 4 minutes.
- Myocardial cells die after only 20 to 30 minutes of ischaemia.
- Fibroblasts within myocardium remain viable even after many hours of ischaemia.

4. **Hypoxaemia.** Abnormally low blood oxygen content, regardless of cause, increases both the likelihood and extent of infarction.


## Write a short note on red infarct / red and white infarct.

**Definition**

- An **infarct** is an area of ischaemic necrosis caused by occlusion of either the arterial supply or the venous drainage.
- Infarcts are classified according to colour and the presence or absence of infection: they are either **red (haemorrhagic)** or **white (anaemic)**, and may be **septic** or **bland**.

**Causes**

| Feature | Red (haemorrhagic) infarct | White (anaemic) infarct |
|---|---|---|
| Occurs with | Venous occlusions (for example testicular torsion); loose, spongy tissues (for example lung) where blood can collect in the infarcted zone; tissues with dual circulations (for example lung and small intestine); tissues previously congested by sluggish venous outflow; reestablished flow to a site of previous arterial occlusion and necrosis (for example following angioplasty) | Arterial occlusions in solid organs with end-arterial circulation (for example heart, spleen, kidney), where tissue density limits seepage of blood from adjoining capillary beds into the necrotic area |
| Course | In the lung, haemorrhagic infarcts are the rule; extravasated red cells are phagocytosed by macrophages, which convert haem iron into **haemosiderin**, and extensive haemorrhage can leave a firm, brown, haemosiderin-rich residuum | Infarcts in organs without a dual blood supply typically become progressively paler and more sharply defined |

**Morphology**

- Infarcts tend to be **wedge-shaped**, with the occluded vessel at the apex and the periphery of the organ forming the base.
- When the base is a serosal surface, there may be an overlying fibrinous exudate resulting from acute inflammation.
- Fresh infarcts are poorly defined and slightly haemorrhagic; over a few days the margins become better defined by a narrow rim of congestion attributable to inflammation.
- The dominant histological characteristic is **coagulative necrosis**.
- It takes 4 to 12 hours for the dead tissue to show microscopic evidence of necrosis; acute inflammation is present along the margins within a few hours and is usually well defined within 1 to 2 days.
- Eventually a reparative response begins in the preserved margins, and most infarcts are ultimately replaced by **scar tissue**.
- The brain is an exception, in that central nervous system infarction results in **liquefactive necrosis**.
- **Septic infarction** occurs when infected cardiac valve vegetations embolise or when microbes seed necrotic tissue; the infarct is converted to an **abscess**.


## What types of infarct are seen in heart and lungs?

| Organ | Type of infarct | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Heart | **White (anaemic)** infarct | Arterial occlusion in a solid organ with end-arterial circulation, where tissue density limits seepage of blood from adjoining capillary beds into the necrotic area |
| Lung | **Red (haemorrhagic)** infarct | Loose, spongy tissue in which blood can collect in the infarcted zone; dual circulation allows blood to flow from an unobstructed parallel supply into the necrotic zone; haemorrhagic infarcts are the rule |


## What are the types of shock?

| Type of shock | Clinical example | Principal mechanisms |
|---|---|---|
| Cardiogenic | Myocardial infarction, ventricular rupture, arrhythmia, cardiac tamponade, pulmonary embolism | Failure of the myocardial pump resulting from intrinsic myocardial damage, extrinsic compression, or obstruction to outflow |
| Hypovolaemic | Fluid loss (for example haemorrhage, vomiting, diarrhoea, burns or trauma) | Inadequate blood or plasma volume |
| Septic | Overwhelming microbial infections (bacterial and fungal) | Activation of cytokine cascades; peripheral vasodilation and pooling of blood; endothelial activation and injury; leucocyte-induced damage; disseminated intravascular coagulation |

Less commonly, shock can occur in the setting of a spinal cord injury (**neurogenic shock**) or an IgE-mediated hypersensitivity reaction (**anaphylactic shock**). In both these forms of shock, acute vasodilation leads to hypotension and tissue hypoperfusion.

## What type of shock occurs in severe burn?

**Hypovolaemic shock.** It results from low cardiac output due to low blood volume, such as can occur with massive haemorrhage or fluid loss from severe burns.

## Why do the skin become cool and pale in the early non-progressive phase of shock?

1. In the early nonprogressive phase, neurohumoral mechanisms maintain cardiac output and blood pressure: baroreceptor reflexes, release of catecholamines and antidiuretic hormone, activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone axis, and generalised sympathetic stimulation.
2. The net effect is tachycardia, **peripheral vasoconstriction** and renal fluid conservation.
3. **Cutaneous vasoconstriction** causes the characteristic "shocky" skin coolness and pallor.
4. Coronary and cerebral vessels are less sensitive to sympathetic signals and maintain relatively normal calibre, blood flow and oxygen delivery. Blood is thus shunted away from the skin to the vital organs such as the heart and brain.
5. Septic shock can initially cause cutaneous vasodilation, so the patient may present with warm, flushed skin.

## Changes that occur in the progressive phase of shock.

1. The progressive stage is characterised by **tissue hypoperfusion** and onset of worsening circulatory and metabolic derangement, including acidosis.
2. In the setting of persistent oxygen deficit, intracellular aerobic respiration is replaced by **anaerobic glycolysis** with excessive production of lactic acid.
3. The resultant metabolic **lactic acidosis** lowers the tissue pH, which blunts the vasomotor response; arterioles dilate and blood begins to pool in the microcirculation.
4. Peripheral pooling worsens the cardiac output and puts endothelial cells at risk for anoxic injury with subsequent **DIC**.
5. With widespread tissue hypoxia, vital organs are affected and begin to fail.

## Define shock.

**Shock** is a state of circulatory failure that impairs tissue perfusion and leads to cellular hypoxia.

It is defined as a state of systemic tissue hypoperfusion resulting from reduced cardiac output, reduced effective circulating blood volume, or both.


## What is anaphylactic shock?

**Anaphylactic shock** is shock that occurs in the setting of an **IgE-mediated hypersensitivity reaction**, in which acute vasodilation leads to hypotension and tissue hypoperfusion.


## Name the stages of shock.

1. **Initial nonprogressive stage:** reflex compensatory mechanisms are activated and vital organ perfusion is maintained.
2. **Progressive stage:** tissue hypoperfusion and onset of worsening circulatory and metabolic derangement, including acidosis.
3. **Irreversible stage:** cellular and tissue injury is so severe that even if the haemodynamic defects are corrected, survival is not possible.


## Write a short note on hemorrhagic shock (pathophysiology of severe hemorrhage after RTA).

**Definition and cause**

- **Hypovolaemic shock** results from low cardiac output due to low blood volume, such as occurs with **massive haemorrhage** or fluid loss from severe burns.
- Shock may also complicate extensive trauma.
- Trauma may trigger systemic inflammation, as in systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS).

**Pathophysiology**

1. **Initial nonprogressive stage.** Reflex compensatory mechanisms maintain cardiac output and blood pressure.

- These mechanisms are baroreceptor reflexes, release of catecholamines and antidiuretic hormone, activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone axis, and generalised sympathetic stimulation.
- The net effect is tachycardia, peripheral vasoconstriction and renal fluid conservation.
- Cutaneous vasoconstriction causes the characteristic "shocky" skin coolness and pallor.
- Coronary and cerebral vessels are less sensitive to sympathetic signals and maintain relatively normal calibre, blood flow and oxygen delivery, so blood is shunted from the skin to the heart and brain.

2. **Progressive stage.** Persistent oxygen deficit causes widespread tissue hypoxia.

- Aerobic respiration is replaced by anaerobic glycolysis, with excessive production of lactic acid.
- The resulting metabolic **lactic acidosis** lowers the tissue pH, which blunts the vasomotor response; arterioles dilate and blood begins to pool in the microcirculation.
- Peripheral pooling worsens the cardiac output and puts endothelial cells at risk of anoxic injury with subsequent **DIC**.
- Vital organs are affected and begin to fail.

3. **Irreversible stage.** Cellular and tissue injury is so severe that survival is not possible even if the haemodynamic defects are corrected.

- Widespread cell injury is reflected in lysosomal enzyme leakage, which further aggravates the shock state.
- Myocardial contractile function worsens, in part because of increased nitric oxide synthesis.
- The ischaemic bowel may allow intestinal flora to enter the circulation, so bacteraemic shock may be superimposed.
- Renal failure commonly follows because of ischaemic injury of the kidney, and the downward spiral culminates in death.

**Morphology**

- The cellular and tissue effects are those of hypoxic injury, caused by a combination of hypoperfusion and microvascular thrombosis.
- The brain, heart, kidneys, adrenals and gastrointestinal tract are most involved.
- Fibrin thrombi are most readily visualised in kidney glomeruli.
- Adrenal cortical cell lipid depletion reflects increased use of stored lipids for steroid synthesis.
- The lungs are resistant to hypoxic injury in hypovolaemic shock occurring after haemorrhage.

**Clinical features**

- Hypotension, weak rapid pulse, tachypnoea, and cool, clammy, cyanotic skin.
- If the patient survives the initial insult, worsening renal function can provoke a phase dominated by progressive oliguria, acidosis and electrolyte imbalances.
- More than 90% of young, otherwise healthy patients with hypovolaemic shock survive with appropriate management.


## Write the pathogenesis/mechanism of septic shock.

**Triggers**

- Septic shock is most frequently triggered by **gram-positive bacterial infections**, followed by gram-negative bacteria and fungi; emerging viral infections such as SARS-CoV-2 can also cause sepsis.
- A variety of microbial constituents, such as **endotoxin**, **staphylococcal enterotoxin B** and **M protein of streptococci**, can trigger the process.
- Macrophages, neutrophils, dendritic cells, endothelial cells and soluble components of the innate immune system (for example complement) recognise and are activated by diverse substances derived from microorganisms.
- Sepsis occurs when the response to infection becomes generalised and injures normal tissues remote from the site of infection; it represents the failure of the normal control mechanisms that contain an infection.

**1. Inflammatory and counter-inflammatory responses**

- Various microbial cell wall constituents engage receptors on cells of the innate immune system, triggering proinflammatory responses.
- Likely initiators are signalling pathways downstream of **Toll-like receptors (TLRs)**, which recognise pathogen-associated molecular patterns (PAMPs) and damage-associated molecular patterns (DAMPs).
- G protein-coupled receptors detect bacterial peptides, and C-type lectin receptors such as Dectins detect fungal antigens.
- Ligation of these receptors increases expression of inflammatory mediator genes, mainly through activation and nuclear translocation of **NF-κB**.
- Upregulated mediators include TNF, IL-1, IL-12, IL-18, interferon-γ and high-mobility group box 1 protein (HMGB1).
- C-reactive protein and procalcitonin are elevated; reactive oxygen species, prostaglandins and platelet-activating factor (PAF) are also elaborated.
- These effector molecules induce endothelial cells to upregulate adhesion molecule expression and further stimulate cytokine and chemokine production.
- The complement cascade is activated by microbial components, producing anaphylatoxins (C3a, C5a), a chemotactic fragment (C5a) and an opsonin (C3b).
- Microbial components activate coagulation directly through factor XII and indirectly through altered endothelial function; widespread thrombin activation further augments inflammation by triggering protease-activated receptors on inflammatory cells.
- The hyperinflammatory state triggers counterregulatory immunosuppressive mechanisms, so septic patients may oscillate between hyperinflammatory and immunosuppressed states.
- Proposed mechanisms of immune suppression are a shift from proinflammatory (Th1) to anti-inflammatory cytokines, anti-inflammatory mediators (soluble TNF receptor, IL-1 receptor antagonist, IL-10), lymphocyte apoptosis, the immunosuppressive effects of apoptotic cells, and induction of cellular anergy.

**2. Endothelial activation and injury**

- The proinflammatory state and endothelial activation lead to widespread vascular leakage and tissue oedema, which impair nutrient delivery and waste removal.
- Inflammatory cytokines loosen endothelial tight junctions, making vessels leaky, with accumulation of protein-rich oedema fluid throughout the body; this may be exacerbated by intravenous fluids.
- Activated endothelium upregulates production of **nitric oxide** and other vasoactive mediators (C3a, C5a, PAF), which contribute to vascular smooth muscle relaxation and systemic hypotension.
- Microvascular dysfunction occurs: capillaries with intermittent flow increase, flow is heterogeneous in different capillary beds, and normal autoregulation of flow is lost, causing a mismatch between oxygen needs and oxygen delivery.

**3. Induction of a procoagulant state**

- The derangement in coagulation produces **DIC** in up to one-half of septic patients.
- Proinflammatory cytokines increase tissue factor production by monocytes and possibly endothelial cells, and decrease endothelial anticoagulant factors: tissue factor pathway inhibitor, thrombomodulin and protein C.
- They dampen fibrinolysis by increasing PAI-1 expression.
- Neutrophil extracellular traps (NETs) may promote the procoagulant state by stimulating both intrinsic and extrinsic pathways of coagulation.
- Vascular leak and tissue oedema decrease blood flow in small vessels, producing stasis and diminishing washout of activated coagulation factors.
- The result is systemic activation of thrombin and deposition of fibrin-rich thrombi in small vessels throughout the body, further compromising tissue perfusion.
- In full-blown DIC, consumption of coagulation factors and platelets is so great that deficiencies appear, leading to concomitant bleeding and haemorrhage.

**4. Metabolic abnormalities**

- Septic patients show **insulin resistance** and **hyperglycaemia**.
- TNF, IL-1, stress-induced hormones (glucagon, growth hormone, glucocorticoids) and catecholamines all drive gluconeogenesis.
- Proinflammatory cytokines suppress insulin release and promote insulin resistance in the liver and other tissues, likely by impairing surface expression of glucose transporter-4 (GLUT-4).
- Blood levels of glucose, triglycerides and lactate rise.
- Hyperglycaemia decreases neutrophil function, suppressing bactericidal activity, and increases adhesion molecule expression on endothelial cells.
- An initial surge in glucocorticoid production may be followed by adrenal insufficiency and a functional glucocorticoid deficit, from depressed synthetic capacity of the adrenals or frank adrenal necrosis resulting from DIC (**Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome** [adrenal haemorrhage in meningococcal septicaemia]).
- Cellular hypoxia and diminished oxidative phosphorylation increase lactate production and cause lactic acidosis.

**5. Organ dysfunction**

- Systemic hypotension, interstitial oedema, microvascular dysfunction and small vessel thrombosis decrease delivery of oxygen and nutrients, and the hypoxic cells fail to use even the nutrients that are delivered.
- Mitochondrial damage resulting from oxidative stress impairs oxygen use.
- High levels of cytokines and secondary mediators diminish myocardial contractility and cardiac output.
- Increased vascular permeability and endothelial injury can lead to acute respiratory distress syndrome.
- Failure of multiple organs, particularly the kidneys, liver, lungs and heart, culminates in death.

**Superantigens**

- Secreted bacterial proteins called superantigens cause a syndrome similar to septic shock (for example toxic shock syndrome).
- Superantigens are polyclonal T-lymphocyte activators that induce release of high levels of cytokines, with manifestations ranging from a diffuse rash to vasodilation, hypotension, shock and death.


## A 40y male lost consciousness with cold clammy skin after severe bleeding in RTA. What is your probable diagnosis?

The probable diagnosis is **hypovolaemic shock** due to massive haemorrhage.

- Hypovolaemic shock results from low cardiac output due to low blood volume, as occurs with massive haemorrhage.
- Patients exhibit hypotension, a weak rapid pulse, tachypnoea, and **cool, clammy, cyanotic skin**.
- Cutaneous vasoconstriction, from the neurohumoral response to falling cardiac output and blood pressure, causes the characteristic "shocky" skin coolness and pallor.


## Same patient: what complications may occur?

If the underlying cause is not corrected, shock passes from the nonprogressive stage to the progressive stage and then to the irreversible stage, with the following complications.

- **Lactic acidosis**: persistent oxygen deficit replaces aerobic respiration with anaerobic glycolysis and excessive lactic acid production.
- **Peripheral pooling** of blood in the microcirculation, which worsens cardiac output.
- **DIC**, because peripheral pooling puts endothelial cells at risk of anoxic injury.
- Failure of vital organs from widespread tissue hypoxia.
- **Lysosomal enzyme leakage**, which further aggravates the shock state.
- Worsening myocardial contractile function, in part because of increased nitric oxide synthesis.
- **Bacteraemic shock** superimposed, because the ischaemic bowel may allow intestinal flora to enter the circulation.
- **Renal failure** from ischaemic injury of the kidney, with a phase of progressive oliguria, acidosis and electrolyte imbalances.
- **Hypoxic injury** of the brain, heart, kidneys, adrenals and gastrointestinal tract; fibrin thrombi form in tissues and are most readily visualised in kidney glomeruli; adrenal cortical cells show lipid depletion.
- Diffuse alveolar damage, the so-called **shock lung**, which trauma can precipitate even though the lungs are resistant to hypoxic injury in hypovolaemic shock after haemorrhage.
- **Multiorgan failure and death.**

Except for neuronal and cardiomyocyte loss, affected tissues can recover completely if the patient survives.


## A 25y male has high fever, sore throat, gum/mandibular swelling after tooth extraction, then collapses with feeble pulse and unrecordable BP. What is your clinical diagnosis?

The clinical diagnosis is **septic shock**.

- Sepsis is life-threatening organ dysfunction caused by a dysregulated host response to infection.
- Septic shock is a subset of sepsis in which particularly profound circulatory, cellular and metabolic abnormalities are associated with a greater risk of mortality than with sepsis alone.
- Activated endothelium upregulates nitric oxide and other vasoactive mediators, which contribute to vascular smooth muscle relaxation and **systemic hypotension**.
- Cytokines loosen endothelial tight junctions, causing widespread vascular leakage and tissue oedema, which impede tissue perfusion.


## Same patient: what may be the etiology?

The aetiology is a **microbial infection** that has become generalised.

- Septic shock is most frequently triggered by **gram-positive bacterial infections**, followed by gram-negative bacteria and fungi.
- A variety of microbial constituents can trigger the process, such as **endotoxin**, **staphylococcal enterotoxin B** and **M protein of streptococci**.
- Superantigens, secreted bacterial proteins that are polyclonal T-lymphocyte activators, cause a similar syndrome (for example toxic shock syndrome).
- Sepsis occurs when the response to infection becomes generalised and injures normal tissues remote from the site of infection.


## Normal serum levels of Na⁺, K⁺, Cl⁻ and HCO₃⁻.

| Electrolyte | Normal serum level |
|---|---|
| **Sodium (Na⁺)** | 135 to 145 mEq/L |
| **Potassium (K⁺)** | 3.7 to 5.2 mEq/L |
| **Chloride (Cl⁻)** | 98 to 107 mEq/L |
| **Bicarbonate (HCO₃⁻)** | 21 to 28 mEq/L |

## What is hyponatraemia?

**Hyponatraemia** is a serum sodium concentration of less than 135 mEq/L. The cut-off can vary to a small extent in different laboratories.

## Causes of hyponatraemia.

**Hypovolaemic hyponatraemia** (total body water decreases less than total body sodium)

- Gastrointestinal fluid loss (diarrhoea or vomiting)
- Third spacing of fluids (pancreatitis, hypoalbuminaemia, small bowel obstruction)
- Diuretics
- Osmotic diuresis (glucose, mannitol)
- Salt-wasting nephropathies
- Cerebral salt-wasting syndrome (urinary salt wasting, possibly caused by increased brain natriuretic peptide)
- Mineralocorticoid deficiency

**Hypervolaemic hyponatraemia** (total body water increases more than total body sodium)

- Renal causes: acute renal failure, chronic renal failure and nephrotic syndrome
- Extrarenal causes: congestive heart failure and cirrhosis
- Iatrogenic causes

**Euvolaemic hyponatraemia** (total body water increases with stable total body sodium)

- Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH)
- Addison disease [primary adrenocortical insufficiency]
- Hypothyroidism
- High fluid intake, as in primary polydipsia or potomania
- Medical testing related to excessive fluids, such as colonoscopy or cardiac catheterisation
- Iatrogenic causes
- Drugs: vasopressin analogues such as desmopressin and oxytocin; medications that stimulate vasopressin release or potentiate its effects; medications that impair urinary dilution, such as thiazide diuretics; medications such as carbamazepine or its analogues; illicit drugs such as methylenedioxymethamphetamine (MDMA or ecstasy)

## What is hypernatraemia?

**Hypernatraemia** is a serum sodium concentration of greater than 145 mEq/L.

## Causes of hypernatraemia.

The basic mechanisms are water deficit and excess solute. Total body water loss relative to solute loss is the most common reason.

**Combined water and solute loss, extrarenal**

- Gastroenteritis, vomiting, prolonged nasogastric drainage and burns
- Excessive sweating from exercise, fever or high heat exposure

**Renal losses**

- Intrinsic renal disease, post-obstructive diuresis, and osmotic or loop diuretics
- Osmotic diuresis from hyperglycaemia or mannitol

**Free water loss**

- Central diabetes insipidus, from inadequate ADH production: idiopathic, head trauma, cranial neoplasm and pituitary infiltrative diseases such as sarcoidosis and histiocytosis
- Nephrogenic diabetes insipidus, from tubular unresponsiveness to ADH: X-linked inherited pattern and medications including lithium, foscarnet and demeclocycline
- Increased insensible loss

**Inadequate fluid intake**

- Breastfed babies, child or elder abuse, and patients with an impaired thirst response

**Excess sodium**

- Iatrogenic causes, improper formula mixing, excess sodium bicarbonate ingestion, salt tablet poisoning, hyperaldosteronism and seawater drowning

## What is hypokalaemia?

**Hypokalaemia** is a serum potassium level below the acceptable lower limit of normal, which is 3.5 mmol/L.

## Causes of hypokalaemia.

**Decreased potassium intake**

- Poor nutrition or restrictive diets
- Eating disorders

**Increased intracellular uptake**

- Insulin injection
- Inhaled β-adrenergic agents

**Increased potassium losses**

- Heavy sweating
- Loop diuretic use
- Diarrhoea
- Vomiting
- Other gastrointestinal losses: tumours, infections, intestinal obstruction, bowel diversion, cancer therapy, chronic laxative abuse, clay (bentonite) ingestion, malabsorption and sodium polystyrene sulfonate

**Renal disorders**

- Chloride-responsive metabolic alkalosis (chloride loss from vomiting or nasogastric suction)
- Chloride-resistant metabolic alkalosis from mineralocorticoid excess: Conn syndrome [primary hyperaldosteronism], congenital adrenal hyperplasia (11-β or 17-α-hydroxylase deficiency), renin-secreting tumours, ectopic ACTH syndrome, Cushing disease [ACTH-secreting pituitary adenoma] or syndrome, hereditary glucocorticoid-responsive aldosteronism, hypertensive emergency and malignant hypertension, renovascular hypertension (renal artery stenosis) and vasculitis
- Genetic conditions: Liddle syndrome [inherited renal sodium-channel overactivity causing hypertension with hypokalaemia], syndrome of apparent mineralocorticoid excess, Gitelman syndrome [inherited distal tubular salt wasting causing hypokalaemic alkalosis] and Bartter syndrome [inherited loop of Henle salt wasting causing hypokalaemic alkalosis]
- Other renal conditions: Fanconi syndrome [generalised proximal tubular reabsorption defect], renal tubular acidosis (especially type 1), hypomagnesaemia, uncontrolled diabetes mellitus and ureterosigmoidostomy

## What is hyperkalaemia?

**Hyperkalaemia** is a serum or plasma potassium level exceeding the upper limit of normal, typically greater than 5.0 to 5.5 mEq/L.

## Causes of hyperkalaemia.

**Pseudohyperkalaemia**

- Haemolysis during specimen collection, which is more likely with a syringe than a vacuum device, prolonged tourniquet use and excessive fist-pumping
- Leucocytosis or thrombocytosis, which can falsely raise the measured potassium

**Increased potassium intake**

- Excessive dietary potassium, which matters mainly when kidney function is impaired
- Intravenous sources: potassium-containing fluids, total parenteral nutrition, potassium-rich medications and massive blood transfusions

**Intracellular potassium shifts**

- Cellular injury from rhabdomyolysis (including crush injury, excessive exercise and haemolytic processes)
- Metabolic acidosis, including acidosis from low effective arterial blood volume due to sepsis or dehydration
- Insulin deficiency, as in diabetic ketoacidosis
- Succinylcholine, particularly in people with upregulated receptors from subacute neuromuscular disease
- Tumour lysis syndrome
- Hyperkalaemic periodic paralysis

**Impaired potassium excretion**

- Acute and chronic kidney disease
- Acute volume depletion from dehydration or haemorrhage
- Reduced effective circulating volume from congestive heart failure or cirrhosis
- Tubular dysfunction, including aldosterone deficiency or insensitivity

## What is hypocalcaemia?

**Hypocalcaemia** is an electrolyte disorder characterised by abnormally low serum calcium levels.

## Causes of hypocalcaemia.

**Parathyroid hormone deficiency**

- Destruction of the parathyroid glands, postsurgical or autoimmune; postsurgical hypoparathyroidism accounts for about 75% of hypoparathyroidism cases
- Abnormal regulation of PTH production and secretion
- Abnormal parathyroid development, including DiGeorge syndrome [22q11.2 deletion with thymic and parathyroid hypoplasia], CHARGE syndrome, gracile bone dysplasia, MELAS and medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency
- Hungry bone syndrome after parathyroid surgery in severe hyperparathyroidism
- Autoimmune hypoparathyroidism, including APECED
- Rare gland destruction from granulomatous disease, haemochromatosis, Wilson disease [hereditary copper accumulation from ATP7B mutation], amyloidosis, aluminium-containing phosphate binders, irradiation, HIV infiltration, nivolumab and L-asparaginase

**High parathyroid hormone levels**

- Vitamin D deficiency (intake or malabsorption, limited sun exposure, liver or kidney disease, impaired activation)
- Chronic kidney disease
- Pseudohypoparathyroidism

**Pharmacological causes**

- Bisphosphonates and denosumab
- Cinacalcet
- Cisplatin, through hypomagnesaemia
- Foscarnet

**Other causes**

- Pseudohypocalcaemia from hypoalbuminaemia
- Acid-base disturbances
- Acute pancreatitis
- Severe sepsis, critical illness and trauma, including COVID-19
- Massive blood transfusion, through citrate binding
- Hypomagnesaemia and hypermagnesaemia
- Acute hyperphosphataemia
- Pregnancy
- Neonatal hypocalcaemia
- Osteoblastic metastasis

## What is hypercalcaemia?

**Hypercalcaemia** is a serum calcium level above the normal range, which is approximately 8.9 to 10.1 mg/dL but varies with the laboratory.

## Causes of hypercalcaemia.

**Parathyroid hormone-mediated**

- Primary hyperparathyroidism, typically from a parathyroid adenoma
- Tertiary hyperparathyroidism, from parathyroid hyperplasia due to chronic overstimulation, most often in renal failure or after renal transplant
- Familial hypocalciuric hypercalcaemia, from a calcium-sensing receptor loss-of-function mutation
- Medications: lithium, teriparatide and abaloparatide

**Non-parathyroid hormone-mediated**

- Medication-induced: thiazide diuretics, excessive calcium carbonate (milk-alkali syndrome) and prolonged retinoic acid use
- Hypercalcaemia of malignancy: tumour-produced parathyroid hormone-related protein, bone metastases, haematological malignancies (Hodgkin [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] and non-Hodgkin lymphoma) and granulomatous diseases (sarcoidosis, tuberculosis)
- Vitamin D toxicity: excess vitamin D or calcitriol supplementation, or excess vitamin D-fortified dairy
- Endocrinopathies: hyperthyroidism, phaeochromocytoma and adrenal insufficiency
- Hypercalcaemia of immobilisation

## What is metabolic acidosis?

**Metabolic acidosis** is characterised by an increase in the hydrogen ion concentration in the systemic circulation, resulting in a serum bicarbonate of less than 24 mEq/L.

## Causes of metabolic acidosis.

**Anion gap metabolic acidosis** (remembered by the mnemonic CAT MUDPILES)

- Cyanide and carbon monoxide poisoning
- Arsenic
- Toluene
- Methanol and metformin
- Uraemia
- Diabetic ketoacidosis
- Paraldehyde
- Iron and isoniazid
- Lactate (a consequence of a condition rather than a separate cause)
- Ethylene glycol
- Salicylates

**Non-gap metabolic acidosis**

- Main causes: diarrhoea and renal tubular acidosis
- Rarer causes: Addison disease [primary adrenocortical insufficiency], ureterosigmoid or pancreatic fistulas, acetazolamide use, and hyperalimentation through the start of total parenteral nutrition

## What is metabolic alkalosis?

**Metabolic alkalosis** is a state in which the pH is elevated to greater than 7.45 because of a metabolic process, with an increase in serum bicarbonate.

## Causes of metabolic alkalosis.

**Chloride-responsive (urine chloride less than 10 mEq/L)**

- Loss of hydrogen through the gastrointestinal tract
- Congenital chloride diarrhoea syndrome
- Contraction alkalosis
- Diuretic therapy
- Post-hypercapnia syndrome
- Cystic fibrosis
- Exogenous alkalotic agent use

**Chloride-resistant (urine chloride greater than 20 mEq/L)**

- Retention of bicarbonate
- Shift of hydrogen into intracellular spaces
- Hyperaldosteronism
- Bartter syndrome [inherited loop of Henle salt wasting causing hypokalaemic alkalosis]
- Gitelman syndrome [inherited distal tubular salt wasting causing hypokalaemic alkalosis]

**Specific causes by mechanism**

- Excess loss of hydrogen ions: gastric lavage; excessive vomiting of gastric contents, including vomiting secondary to pyloric stenosis; chronic diarrhoea; congenital chloridorrhoea
- Increased bicarbonate in the extracellular compartment: excess enteral bicarbonate or alkali intake (milk-alkali syndrome); increased parenteral citrate, acetate or lactate; large-volume transfusion of sodium citrate-anticoagulated fresh frozen plasma
- Increased renal reabsorption of bicarbonate: primary hyperaldosteronism, renin-secreting tumours, renal artery stenosis, exogenous mineralocorticoids, congenital adrenal hyperplasia, Liddle syndrome [inherited renal sodium-channel overactivity causing hypertension with hypokalaemia], Cushing syndrome [glucocorticoid excess] and Cushing disease [ACTH-secreting pituitary adenoma]

## Define gene.

A **gene** is the basic unit of heredity, consisting of a segment of DNA arranged in a linear manner along a chromosome; it codes for a specific protein, a segment of protein, or noncoding RNA.


## What is mutation?

A **mutation** is defined as a permanent change in DNA sequence.


## Types of mutation.

1. **Point mutations within coding sequences.** A point mutation is a change in which a single nucleotide base pair is substituted with a different base pair.

- **Missense mutation:** the change alters the code in a triplet of bases and leads to the replacement of one amino acid by another.
- A **conservative** missense mutation replaces the amino acid with a biochemically similar one and may cause little change in protein function.
- A **nonconservative** missense mutation replaces the normal amino acid with a biochemically different one, as in sickle cell anaemia.
- **Nonsense mutation:** the change converts an amino acid codon into a chain-terminating stop codon, as in β-thalassaemia.

2. **Mutations within noncoding sequences.**

- Point mutations or deletions involving promoter and enhancer sequences may interfere with binding of transcription factors, leading to a marked reduction in or total lack of transcription, as in certain forms of thalassaemia.
- Point mutations within introns may lead to defective mRNA splicing.

3. **Deletions and insertions.**

- If the number of base pairs involved is three or a multiple of three, the reading frame remains intact and an abnormal protein lacking or gaining one or more amino acids is synthesised.
- If the number of affected coding bases is not a multiple of three, the reading frame is altered, a **frameshift** mutation, producing incorrect amino acids followed by truncation from a premature stop codon.

4. **Structural alterations in protein-coding genes.** These are copy number changes (amplifications or deletions) or rearrangements that result in aberrant gain or loss of protein function; they may be germline and heritable or somatically acquired, for example the Philadelphia chromosome [t(9;22) producing the BCR-ABL1 fusion] translocation in chronic myeloid leukaemia.

5. **Trinucleotide-repeat mutations.** These are amplifications of simple tandem repeat sequences of three nucleotides, for example 250 to 4000 CGG repeats in the FMR1 gene in fragile X syndrome (the normal average is 29); they are dynamic.


## Two examples of point mutation.

| Example | Type | Change | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Sickle cell anaemia** | Nonconservative missense mutation in the β-globin chain | The triplet CTC (GAG in mRNA, encoding glutamic acid) is changed to CAC (GUG in mRNA, encoding valine) | A single amino acid substitution promotes abnormal haemoglobin polymerisation |
| **β-thalassaemia** | Nonsense mutation in the β-globin gene | The codon for glutamine (CAG) becomes a stop codon (UAG) when U is substituted for C | Premature termination of translation; the short peptide is rapidly degraded, and the deficiency of β-globin chains gives a hereditary anaemia |


## Genetic vs hereditary vs familial vs congenital disorder (differences).

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Genetic** | Disease underlain by genetic alterations: inherited disorders, spontaneously occurring constitutional genetic diseases, and conditions driven by somatically acquired mutations |
| **Hereditary** | Transmitted in the germline through generations |
| **Familial** | Hereditary disorders are, by definition, transmitted through generations and therefore are familial |
| **Congenital** | Simply means "born with" |

- Some congenital diseases are not genetic, for example congenital syphilis.
- Not all genetic diseases are congenital; individuals with Huntington disease [CAG-repeat autosomal dominant neurodegeneration] begin to manifest their condition in adulthood.


## Classify genetic disorders.

| Category | Features |
|---|---|
| **Monogenic (Mendelian) disorders** | Mutations in single genes with large effects; highly penetrant phenotypes; usually follow the classic Mendelian pattern of inheritance |
| **Chromosomal disorders** | Structural or numerical alterations in the autosomes and sex chromosomes; altered copy number of a region that may encompass many genes |
| **Complex multigenic (multifactorial) disorders** | Interactions between multiple genetic variants (polymorphisms) and environmental factors; risk conferred by any single haplotype is low; include atherosclerosis, diabetes mellitus, hypertension, autoimmune diseases and cancers |
| **Single-gene disorders with nonclassical inheritance** | Triplet-repeat mutations, mutations in mitochondrial DNA, and disorders influenced by genomic imprinting or gonadal mosaicism |
| **Disorders from somatically acquired mutations** | Some drive malignancies; others contribute to clonal selection (clonal haematopoiesis of indeterminate potential); others cause overgrowth syndromes (somatic mosaicism) |


## One example of each type of genetic disorder.

| Type | Example |
|---|---|
| **Monogenic (Mendelian)** | Marfan syndrome [inherited fibrillin-1 connective tissue disorder]; familial hypercholesterolaemia |
| **Chromosomal** | Down syndrome [trisomy 21] |
| **Complex multigenic** | Type 2 diabetes mellitus; atherosclerosis |
| **Nonclassical single-gene: trinucleotide-repeat** | Fragile X syndrome |
| Nonclassical single-gene: mitochondrial gene mutation | Leber hereditary optic neuropathy [mitochondrial DNA mutation causing optic nerve degeneration] |
| **Nonclassical single-gene: genomic imprinting** | Prader-Willi syndrome [imprinting disorder from loss of paternal 15q11-13 with hypotonia and obesity] and Angelman syndrome [imprinting disorder from loss of maternal 15q11-13 with ataxia and seizures] |
| **Somatically acquired mutation** | PIK3CA-related somatic overgrowth syndromes |


## Transmission patterns of single-gene disorders.

Single-gene disorders typically follow one of three patterns of inheritance.

1. **Autosomal dominant.**

- Manifested in the heterozygous state.
- Males and females are affected equally, and both sexes can directly transmit the disorder.

2. **Autosomal recessive.**

- Occurs when mutated alleles are inherited from both parents.
- Heterozygotes are unaffected carriers.
- Males and females are affected equally.

3. **X-linked.**

- Caused by abnormalities in genes on the X chromosome, so males are affected whereas females are carriers.
- **X-linked recessive** disorders are typically transmitted by asymptomatic, heterozygous female carriers to their hemizygous sons, who manifest the disease.
- **X-linked dominant** disorders are transmitted by an affected heterozygous female to half her sons and half her daughters, or by an affected male parent to all his daughters but none of his sons.


## Criteria of autosomal dominant disorders.

- Manifested in the **heterozygous state**, so one parent of an index case is usually affected.
- Both **males and females** are affected, and both can transmit the condition.
- When an affected and an unaffected person reproduce, each child has **one chance in two** of inheriting the mutated allele and developing the disease.

Exceptions to these basic rules:

- Patients may not have an affected parent if the condition is caused by a new (**de novo**) germline mutation.
- If a disease markedly reduces reproductive fitness, more cases result from new mutations.
- New mutations are known to have a propensity to occur in germ cells of relatively older fathers (for example mutations in FGFR2 and FGFR3, which cause Apert syndrome [FGFR2-related craniosynostosis with syndactyly] and achondroplasia).
- Patients may not have an affected parent if the disease is partially penetrant; this is **incomplete penetrance**, in which some individuals inherit the mutant gene but are phenotypically normal.
- **Variable expressivity** is when a trait is manifest in all individuals carrying the mutant gene but at differing degrees of severity, for example neurofibromatosis type 1.
- In some conditions the age at onset is delayed, as in Huntington disease [CAG-repeat autosomal dominant neurodegeneration].


## Two examples of autosomal dominant disorders.

| Disorder | Inheritance |
|---|---|
| **Marfan syndrome** [inherited fibrillin-1 connective tissue disorder] | A disorder of connective tissues caused by an inherited defect in fibrillin-1 (FBN1 gene); approximately 75% of cases are familial and transmitted by autosomal dominant inheritance; the remainder are sporadic and arise from de novo mutations |
| **Familial hypercholesterolaemia** | An autosomal dominant disorder caused by mutations in the genes encoding the LDL receptor (85% of cases), the ApoB protein (5% to 10% of cases) or PCSK9 (1% to 2% of cases) |


## Criteria of autosomal recessive disorders.

- Both alleles of an autosomal gene must be mutated to produce a phenotypic manifestation.
- The trait does not usually affect the parents of the affected individual (they "skip generations"), but **siblings** may show the disease.
- Siblings have **one chance in four** of having the trait; the recurrence risk is 25% for each birth.
- Males and females are affected equally.
- Affected individuals may inherit two distinct loss-of-function mutations (**compound heterozygotes**), or the same mutant allele both maternally and paternally.
- If the mutation has a low allele frequency in the general population, the affected homozygous individual (proband) is very likely the product of a **consanguineous marriage**.
- The expression of the defect tends to be more **uniform** than in autosomal dominant disorders.
- **Complete penetrance** is common.
- Onset is frequently **early in life**.
- New (de novo) mutations occur, but the individual with a new mutation is an asymptomatic heterozygote, so several generations may pass before a descendant has an affected child with another heterozygote parent.
- Many of the mutated genes encode **enzymes**; heterozygote carriers, with half the usual complement of enzyme function, function normally.
- They include almost all inborn errors of metabolism.


## Two examples of autosomal recessive disorders.

| Disorder | Inheritance |
|---|---|
| **Gaucher disease** [glucocerebrosidase deficiency, a lysosomal storage disease] | An autosomal recessive disorder resulting from mutations in the gene encoding glucocerebrosidase; it is the most common lysosomal storage disorder |
| **Mucopolysaccharidoses** | All are inherited as autosomal recessive traits except Hunter syndrome [X-linked iduronate sulfatase deficiency, mucopolysaccharidosis II], which is an X-linked recessive trait |


## What are the features of autosomal dominant inheritance?

- Manifested in the **heterozygous state**, so one parent of an index case is usually affected.
- Both **males and females** are affected, and both can transmit the condition.
- When an affected and an unaffected person reproduce, each child has **one chance in two** of inheriting the mutated allele and developing the disease.

Exceptions to these basic rules:

- Patients may not have an affected parent if the condition is caused by a new (**de novo**) germline mutation.
- If a disease markedly reduces reproductive fitness, more cases result from new mutations.
- New mutations are known to have a propensity to occur in germ cells of relatively older fathers (for example mutations in FGFR2 and FGFR3, which cause Apert syndrome [FGFR2-related craniosynostosis with syndactyly] and achondroplasia).
- **Incomplete penetrance:** some individuals inherit the mutant gene but are phenotypically normal; 50% penetrance indicates that 50% of those who carry the gene express the trait.
- **Variable expressivity:** the trait is manifest in all individuals carrying the mutant gene but at differing degrees of severity, for example neurofibromatosis type 1.
- The age at onset may be delayed, as in Huntington disease [CAG-repeat autosomal dominant neurodegeneration].


## What are the main features of X-linked recessive inheritance?

- X-linked disorders are caused by abnormalities in genes on the X chromosome, so **males are affected** whereas **females are carriers**.
- The X chromosome contains many genes without corresponding loci on the Y chromosome.
- Females are asymptomatic **heterozygous carriers**; affected males are **hemizygous** for the X-linked mutant gene.
- An affected male does **not transmit the disorder to his sons**, who inherit a paternal Y chromosome.
- All the daughters of an affected male are **obligate carriers**.
- Daughters of heterozygous women have one chance in two of being a carrier.
- Sons of heterozygous women have one chance in two of inheriting the mutant allele and being affected.
- Heterozygous females are generally unaffected because of their normal allele.
- X inactivation in females results in mosaicism for cells in which the mutant X chromosome is active; the inactivation may be random or skewed to favour activity of the X chromosome with the wild-type allele.
- Example: **glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency** is expressed principally in males, and the severity of the haemolytic reaction is almost always less in heterozygous women than in hemizygous men.


## Write a short note on autosomal recessive inheritance.

**Definition**

- In autosomal recessive traits, both alleles of an autosomal gene must be mutated to produce a phenotypic manifestation.
- They make up the largest category of Mendelian disorders.

**Features**

- The trait does not usually affect the parents of the affected individual (they "skip generations"), but **siblings** may show the disease.
- Siblings have **one chance in four** of having the trait; the recurrence risk is 25% for each birth.
- Males and females are affected equally.
- Affected individuals may be **compound heterozygotes**, inheriting two distinct loss-of-function mutations, or may inherit the same mutant allele both maternally and paternally.
- If the mutation has a low allele frequency in the general population, the affected homozygous individual (proband) is very likely the product of a **consanguineous marriage**.
- The expression of the defect tends to be more **uniform** than in autosomal dominant disorders.
- **Complete penetrance** is common.
- Onset is frequently **early in life**.
- Several generations may pass before a descendant of a de novo mutation carrier has an affected child, because the individual with a new mutation is an asymptomatic heterozygote.

**Molecular basis**

- Many of the mutated genes encode **enzymes**.
- In heterozygote carriers, cells with half the usual complement of the enzyme function normally.
- Autosomal recessive disorders include almost all inborn errors of metabolism.

**Examples**

- **Gaucher disease** [glucocerebrosidase deficiency, a lysosomal storage disease], from mutations in the gene encoding glucocerebrosidase.
- **Mucopolysaccharidoses**, all of which are autosomal recessive except Hunter syndrome [X-linked iduronate sulfatase deficiency, mucopolysaccharidosis II].


## Types of chromosomal disorders.

Chromosomal (cytogenetic) disorders take the form of an abnormal number of chromosomes or alterations in the structure of one or more chromosomes.

| Type | Details |
|---|---|
| **Numerical abnormalities** | Any exact multiple of the haploid number (23) is **euploid**; a chromosome complement that is not an exact multiple of 23 is **aneuploidy**, usually due to nondisjunction or anaphase lag; the result is **trisomy** (2n plus 1) or **monosomy** (2n minus 1) |
| **Structural abnormalities** | Usually result from chromosome breakage followed by loss or rearrangement of material: translocation, isochromosome, inversion, deletion and ring chromosome |
| **Mosaicism** | Two or more populations of cells with different chromosomal complements in the same individual, from mitotic errors in early development |

They may involve the autosomes or the sex chromosomes.


## Examples of chromosomal / cytogenetic disorders.

| Group | Disorder | Chromosomal abnormality |
|---|---|---|
| Autosomal | **Down syndrome** [trisomy 21] | Trisomy 21 |
| Autosomal | **Edwards syndrome** [trisomy 18] | Trisomy 18 |
| Autosomal | **Patau syndrome** [trisomy 13] | Trisomy 13 |
| Autosomal | **22q11.2 deletion syndrome** (DiGeorge syndrome [22q11.2 deletion with thymic and parathyroid hypoplasia] and velocardiofacial syndrome) | Small deletion of band q11.2 on the long arm of chromosome 22 |
| Sex chromosomes | **Klinefelter syndrome** [47,XXY] | Two or more X chromosomes and one or more Y chromosomes; classically 47,XXY |
| Sex chromosomes | **Turner syndrome** [45,X monosomy] | Complete or partial monosomy of the X chromosome; classically 45,X |


## Classic karyotype of trisomy 21.

- The classic karyotype is **47,XX,+21** in a female and **47,XY,+21** in a male.
- Approximately 95% of affected individuals have trisomy 21, so their chromosome count is 47.
- FISH with chromosome 21-specific probes reveals the extra copy of chromosome 21.
- The most common cause is meiotic nondisjunction, and the parents of such children have a normal karyotype.


## Disorders associated with alteration in the number of chromosomes.

| Disorder | Karyotype | Alteration |
|---|---|---|
| **Down syndrome** [trisomy 21] | 47,XX,+21 or 47,XY,+21 | Trisomy 21 |
| **Edwards syndrome** [trisomy 18] | Trisomy 18 | Extra copy of chromosome 18 |
| **Patau syndrome** [trisomy 13] | Trisomy 13 | Extra copy of chromosome 13 |
| **Klinefelter syndrome** [47,XXY] | 47,XXY in 90% of cases | Extra X chromosome |
| **Turner syndrome** [45,X monosomy] | 45,X in approximately 57% of cases | Monosomy of the X chromosome |

- Other trisomies involving chromosomes 8, 9, 13, 18 and 22 have been described.
- Monosomy involving an autosome generally causes loss of too much genetic information to permit live birth or even embryogenesis.
- Except for trisomy 21, all autosomal trisomies produce severe malformations and almost invariably death at an early age.


## Complications of Down syndrome.

1. **Congenital heart disease** occurs in approximately 40% of patients.

- Atrioventricular septal defects account for 43% of cases.
- Ventricular septal defects account for 32%.
- Atrial septal defects account for 19%.
- Tetralogy of Fallot [ventricular septal defect, overriding aorta, pulmonary stenosis and right ventricular hypertrophy] accounts for 6%.
- Cardiac problems are responsible for most of the deaths in infancy and early childhood.

2. **Other congenital malformations**, including atresias of the oesophagus and small bowel, are common.
3. **Leukaemia:** there is a 20-fold increased risk of precursor B cell acute lymphoblastic leukaemia and a 500-fold increased risk of acute myeloid leukaemias.
4. **Alzheimer disease [progressive dementia with amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles]:** virtually all patients older than age 40 develop neuropathological changes characteristic of Alzheimer disease.
5. **Infections and autoimmunity:** abnormal immune responses predispose to serious infections, particularly of the lungs, and to thyroid autoimmunity.


## Structural alterations of chromosomes in cytogenetic disorders.

Structural changes usually result from chromosome breakage followed by loss or rearrangement of material.

| Alteration | Description |
|---|---|
| **Balanced reciprocal translocation** | Single breaks in each of two chromosomes with exchange of material; no or very little loss of genetic material, so the carrier is likely phenotypically normal but at increased risk of producing abnormal gametes |
| **Robertsonian translocation (centric fusion)** | Translocation between two acrocentric chromosomes (13, 14, 15, 21, 22), with breaks close to the centromeres; one very large chromosome and one extremely small chromosome result, and the small product is usually lost |
| **Isochromosome** | One arm of a chromosome is lost and the remaining arm is duplicated; the most common in live births is the isochromosome of the long arm of the X chromosome, i(X)(q10) |
| **Inversion** | Two breaks within a single chromosome followed by reinsertion of the inverted intervening segment; **paracentric** if confined to one arm, **pericentric** if the breaks are on opposite sides of the centromere |
| **Deletion** | Loss of a portion of a chromosome; **interstitial** (two breaks within a chromosome arm) or **terminal** (a single break) |
| **Ring chromosome** | A special form of deletion, produced when a break occurs at both ends of a chromosome with fusion of the damaged ends |


## What do you mean by karyotype?

The study of chromosomes is called **karyotyping**. A **karyotype** is obtained by arranging each pair of autosomes according to length, followed by the sex chromosomes.


## Normal genotype of a male and a female.

| Sex | Normal chromosome complement |
|---|---|
| **Male** | 46,XY |
| **Female** | 46,XX |

Human somatic cells contain 46 chromosomes: 22 homologous pairs of autosomes and two sex chromosomes, XX in the female and XY in the male.


## What are the cytogenetic disorders?

**Cytogenetic disorders** may take the form of an abnormal number of chromosomes or alterations in the structure of one or more chromosomes.


## Write a short note on Down's syndrome.

**Definition**

- **Down syndrome** [trisomy 21] is the most common chromosomal disorder and a major cause of intellectual disability.
- It is associated with an extra copy of genes on chromosome 21.
- The incidence in newborns in the United States is about 1 in 700.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Approximately 95% of affected individuals have **trisomy 21**, with a chromosome count of 47; the most common cause is **meiotic nondisjunction**, and the parents have a normal karyotype.
- In about 4% of cases the extra chromosomal material derives from a **Robertsonian translocation** of the long arm of chromosome 21 to another acrocentric chromosome (for example 22 or 14).
- Such translocation cases are sometimes, but not always, familial; the translocated chromosome is inherited from one of the parents, usually the mother, who is a carrier.
- Approximately 1% of patients are **mosaics**, with a mixture of cells with 46 or 47 chromosomes, from mitotic nondisjunction of chromosome 21 during early embryogenesis; symptoms are variably milder.
- Maternal age has a strong influence on the incidence of trisomy 21: once in 1550 live births in females under age 20 and 1 in 25 live births for mothers older than age 45.
- In 95% of cases the extra chromosome 21 is of maternal origin.
- In translocation or mosaic Down syndrome, maternal age is of no importance.
- Down syndrome results from gene dosage effects at many loci; most protein-coding genes on chromosome 21 are overexpressed, including the gene for amyloid-beta precursor protein (APP).
- Approximately 10% of the overexpressed genes are involved in regulation of mitochondrial functions.

**Clinical features**

- Flat facial profile, oblique palpebral fissures and epicanthic folds, usually evident even at birth.
- Intellectual disability; some mosaics have normal or near-normal intelligence.
- Congenital heart disease in approximately 40% of patients, atresias of the oesophagus and small bowel, increased risk of leukaemia, serious infections, thyroid autoimmunity, and Alzheimer disease [progressive dementia with amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles] in virtually all patients older than age 40.

**Diagnosis**

- FISH with chromosome 21-specific probes reveals the extra copy of chromosome 21.
- Approximately 5% to 10% of the total cell-free DNA in maternal blood is derived from the foetus; next-generation sequencing of it is a noninvasive screening test, confirmed by conventional karyotyping.

**Prognosis**

- The median age at death is 47 years (up from 25 years in 1983).


## Write a short note on Barr body.

**Definition**

The inactive X chromosome can be seen in the interphase nucleus as a darkly staining small mass in contact with the nuclear membrane, known as the **Barr body** [inactivated X chromosome seen as nuclear chromatin], or **X chromatin**.

**Lyon hypothesis** [random inactivation of one X chromosome in females] (Mary Lyon, 1961)

1. Only one of the X chromosomes is genetically active.
2. The other X chromosome, of either maternal or paternal origin, undergoes **heteropyknosis** and is rendered inactive.
3. Inactivation of either the maternal or the paternal X chromosome occurs at random among all the cells of the blastocyst on or about day 5.5 of embryonic life.
4. Inactivation of the same X chromosome persists in all the cells derived from each precursor cell.

**Significance**

- The great preponderance of normal females are **mosaics**, with two populations of cells, one with an inactivated maternal X chromosome and the other with an inactivated paternal X chromosome.
- This explains why females have the same dosage of X-linked active genes as males.
- In XY males there is no X inactivation.

**Molecular basis**

- The long noncoding RNA **XIST** is retained in the nucleus, where it "coats" the X chromosome that it is transcribed from and initiates gene silencing by chromatin modification and DNA methylation.
- XIST expression is switched off in the active X chromosome.
- A subset of genes escape X inactivation: 30% of genes on Xp and 3% on Xq.
- The pseudoautosomal regions at the tips of the X and Y chromosomes also escape X inactivation.


## 10-year-old boy with very low IQ, flat facial profile, single palmar crease, stunted growth — Probable genetic diagnosis? Haematological malignancy that may occur in future?

**Probable genetic diagnosis:** **Down syndrome [trisomy 21]**.

- The diagnostic clinical features are flat facial profile, oblique palpebral fissures and epicanthic folds.
- Down syndrome is a leading cause of intellectual disability.
- Approximately 95% of cases have trisomy 21, with a chromosome count of 47.

**Haematological malignancy:** **leukaemia**.

- There is a 20-fold increased risk of precursor B cell acute lymphoblastic leukaemia.
- There is a 500-fold increased risk of acute myeloid leukaemias.


## Adolescent girl with primary amenorrhoea, webbing of neck, broad chest, underdeveloped breasts — Likely diagnosis? Karyotype?

**Likely diagnosis:** **Turner syndrome** [45,X monosomy].

- It results from complete or partial monosomy of the X chromosome and is characterised by hypogonadism in phenotypic females.
- Turner syndrome is the single most important cause of primary amenorrhoea, accounting for approximately one-third of the cases.
- At puberty there is failure to develop normal secondary sex characteristics, with reduced breast development.

**Karyotype:**

- Approximately 57% are missing an entire X chromosome, giving the classic **45,X** karyotype.
- Approximately 14% have structural abnormalities of the X chromosome, such as 46,X,i(X)(q10), 46,X,r(X) or deletions of part of the short or long arm.
- Approximately 29% are mosaics, such as 45,X/46,XX.


## A girl has flat facial profile, stunted growth, mental retardation (± simian crease). What is your probable diagnosis?

The probable diagnosis is **Down syndrome [trisomy 21]**.

- The diagnostic clinical features are flat facial profile, oblique palpebral fissures and epicanthic folds, usually evident even at birth.
- Down syndrome is a leading cause of intellectual disability.
- It is the most common chromosomal disorder.


## Same patient: how will you diagnose / what is the karyotype?

**Karyotype**

- In approximately 95% of cases the karyotype is **trisomy 21**, with a chromosome count of 47 (47,XX,+21 in a girl).
- In about 4% an extra copy of chromosome 21 derives from a Robertsonian translocation to another acrocentric chromosome (for example 22 or 14).
- Approximately 1% are mosaics, with cells having 46 or 47 chromosomes.

**Diagnosis**

- Conventional **karyotyping** of cells demonstrates the extra chromosome 21.
- **FISH** with chromosome 21-specific probes reveals the extra copy of chromosome 21.
- Prenatally, next-generation sequencing of cell-free foetal DNA in maternal blood is a noninvasive screening test, and a positive screening test is confirmed by conventional karyotyping of cells obtained by chorionic villus sampling or amniocentesis.


## An adolescent girl has primary amenorrhoea, webbed neck, broad chest, underdeveloped breasts. What is the likely diagnosis?

The likely diagnosis is **Turner syndrome** [45,X monosomy].

- It results from complete or partial monosomy of the X chromosome and is characterised by hypogonadism in phenotypic females.
- Turner syndrome is the single most important cause of primary amenorrhoea, accounting for approximately one-third of the cases.
- Typical features are short stature, webbing of the neck, cubitus valgus, cardiovascular malformations, amenorrhoea, lack of secondary sex characteristics and fibrotic (streak) ovaries.


## Same patient: what is the karyotype?

- Approximately 57% have a **45,X** karyotype, missing an entire X chromosome (the classic karyotype).
- Approximately 14% have structural abnormalities of the X chromosome: an isochromosome of the long arm, 46,X,i(X)(q10); a ring chromosome, 46,X,r(X); or deletion of part of the short or long arm, 46,X,del(Xq) or 46,X,del(Xp).
- Approximately 29% are mosaics with a 45,X cell population, for example 45,X/46,XX, 45,X/46,XY, 45,X/47,XXX or 45,X/46,X,i(X)(q10).


## Outline of laboratory diagnosis of genetic disorders.

1. **Karyotyping** for chromosomal disorders.

- Dividing cells are arrested in metaphase with mitotic spindle inhibitors and stained.
- Chromosomes are identified by G banding (Giemsa stain [Romanowsky-type blood stain]), with approximately 400 to 800 bands per haploid set.

2. **Fluorescence in situ hybridisation (FISH)** uses labelled DNA probes that bind to particular chromosome intervals.

- It detects aneuploidy and microdeletions not detectable by karyotyping.

3. **Array technology** surveys the genome without prior knowledge of the altered region.

- Array-based comparative genomic hybridisation detects copy number gains and losses.
- Single nucleotide polymorphism arrays detect copy number abnormalities and also loss of heterozygosity.

4. **Polymerase chain reaction (PCR)**, followed by:

- Sanger sequencing.
- Restriction fragment length analysis.
- Amplicon length analysis, for example for trinucleotide repeats in fragile X syndrome.
- Real-time PCR.

5. **Next-generation sequencing:** targeted sequencing, whole-exome sequencing and whole-genome sequencing.
6. **Polymorphic markers** (linkage analysis), when the gene responsible is not known or testing for the primary defect is not feasible.
7. **Epigenetic analysis** of methylation, for example in fragile X syndrome, Prader-Willi syndrome [imprinting disorder from loss of paternal 15q11-13 with hypotonia and obesity] and Angelman syndrome [imprinting disorder from loss of maternal 15q11-13 with ataxia and seizures].
8. **Southern blot analysis** for very large trinucleotide expansions that cannot be amplified by conventional PCR.


## Samples used for (prenatal and postnatal) diagnosis of genetic disorders.

| Period | Sample |
|---|---|
| **Prenatal** | Cells obtained by **amniocentesis**; **chorionic villus** biopsy; **umbilical cord blood**; cell-free foetal DNA in maternal blood (as much as 10% of the free DNA in a pregnant mother's blood is of foetal origin), used as a noninvasive screening test |
| **Postnatal** | **Peripheral blood** DNA, most commonly, targeted on clinical suspicion |
