- A positive noninvasive screening test for Down syndrome [trisomy 21] is confirmed by conventional karyotyping of cells obtained by sampling of chorionic villi or by amniocentesis.
- FISH can be performed on prenatal samples and peripheral blood cells.


## Write a short note on karyotype / karyotyping.

**Definition**

- The study of chromosomes, **karyotyping**, is the basic tool of the cytogeneticist.
- A **karyotype** is obtained by arranging each pair of autosomes according to length, followed by the sex chromosomes.

**Procedure**

- Dividing cells are arrested in **metaphase** with mitotic spindle inhibitors (for example N-diacetyl-N-methylcolchicine) and the chromosomes are stained.
- In a metaphase spread, each chromosome takes the form of two chromatids connected at the centromere.
- The most useful staining method is the **Giemsa stain** [Romanowsky-type blood stain], hence **G banding**; it shows distinctive patterns of alternating light and dark bands.
- With standard G banding, approximately 400 to 800 bands per haploid set of chromosomes can be detected.
- Obtaining the cells in prophase gives elongated chromosomes and as many as 1500 bands per karyotype.
- This permits identification of each chromosome and roughly delineates breakpoints and other gross alterations.

**Normal karyotype**

- Human somatic cells contain 46 chromosomes: 22 homologous pairs of autosomes and two sex chromosomes.
- The normal complement is **46,XX** for females and **46,XY** for males.

**Notation**

- The total number of chromosomes is given first, then the sex chromosome complement, then the abnormalities in ascending numerical order; a male with trisomy 21 is 47,XY,+21.
- The short arm is designated **p** (petit) and the long arm **q**.
- Each arm is divided into regions numbered from the centromere outward, and each region into bands and sub-bands; Xp21.2 is the short arm of the X chromosome, region 2, band 1, sub-band 2.

**Limitations**

- To be visible by routine banding, a structural change must involve approximately 2 to 4 million base pairs.
- Fluorescence in situ hybridisation (FISH) has much higher resolution and can detect changes as small as kilobases.


## Define immunity.

The classic definition of **immunity** is protection from infectious pathogens.

Immunity in its broader sense includes host reactions against cancers (tumour immunity), tissue transplants, and even self antigens (autoimmunity).


## Types of immunity.

The mechanisms of immunity fall into two broad categories.

| Feature | Innate immunity | Adaptive immunity |
|---|---|---|
| Other names | Natural, or native, immunity | Acquired, or specific, immunity |
| Nature | Intrinsic mechanisms poised to react immediately; the first line of defence | Mechanisms stimulated by exposure to microbes and other foreign substances |
| Speed | Immediate | Develops more slowly but is even more powerful in combating infections |
| Mediated by | Cells and molecules that recognise products of microbes and dead cells | Lymphocytes and their products, including antibodies |
| Specificity and memory | Not specific for particular microbial antigens; little or no memory | Highly specific receptors for a vast array of antigens; immunologic memory |

Adaptive immunity is of two types.

- **Humoral immunity** is mediated by B lymphocytes and their secreted products, antibodies (immunoglobulins), and protects against extracellular microbes and their toxins.
- **Cell-mediated (cellular) immunity** is mediated by T lymphocytes and is responsible for defence against intracellular microbes and against cancers.


## Write a short note on MHC.

- **Function.** MHC molecules display peptide fragments of protein antigens for recognition by antigen-specific T cells. They were discovered as products of genes that evoke rejection of transplanted organs, and the name derives from their role in determining tissue compatibility between individuals.
- **HLA.** In humans the MHC molecules are called **human leucocyte antigens (HLA)**, because they were initially detected on leucocytes. The genes encoding them are clustered on a small segment of **chromosome 6**.
- **Polymorphism.** The HLA system is highly polymorphic, with thousands of distinct MHC alleles in humans, so everyone's HLA alleles differ from those inherited by most other individuals. This is a formidable barrier in organ transplantation, because grafts exchanged between individuals other than identical twins are recognised as foreign and attacked by the immune system.
- **Haplotype.** The combination of HLA alleles found on a single chromosome is called the HLA haplotype. Each haplotype is inherited as a block, with one set of HLA genes from each parent, so the chance that siblings will have the same MHC is **1 in 4**. This is why siblings are screened first as potential donors for kidney or haematopoietic stem cell transplantation.

| Feature | MHC class I | MHC class II |
|---|---|---|
| Genes | HLA-A, HLA-B and HLA-C | HLA-D region, with three subregions: HLA-DP, HLA-DQ and HLA-DR |
| Structure | Heterodimer of a polymorphic α (heavy) chain, 44 kD, linked to a smaller nonpolymorphic protein, β₂-microglobulin, 12 kD | Heterodimer of a noncovalently associated α chain and β chain, both polymorphic |
| Peptide-binding cleft | Formed by the α₁ and α₂ domains | Formed by the α₁ and β₁ domains |
| Cells expressing it | All nucleated cells and platelets | Mainly cells that present ingested antigens and respond to T-cell help: macrophages, B lymphocytes and dendritic cells |
| Peptides displayed | Derived from cytosolic proteins, including protein antigens of viruses and tumours; degraded in proteasomes and transported into the endoplasmic reticulum, where they bind newly synthesised class I molecules | Derived from extracellular microbes and proteins internalised into endosomes or lysosomes, where they are digested and associate with class II heterodimers |
| Coreceptor | The nonpolymorphic α₃ domain binds **CD8**, so **CD8⁺ cytotoxic T lymphocytes** recognise the peptide only with class I (MHC class I restricted) | The β₂ domain binds **CD4**, so **CD4⁺ helper T cells** recognise the peptide only with class II (MHC class II restricted) |

- **Role in T-cell responses.** MHC molecules let T cells detect antigens that are inside cells (microbial and tumour antigens). By segregating cytosolic and internalised antigens, they ensure that the correct response is mounted: killing of cells harbouring cytosolic microbes and tumour antigens by cytotoxic T lymphocytes, and helper T cell–mediated antibody production and macrophage activation against extracellular and phagocytosed microbes.
- **Immune response depends on the inherited MHC.** An individual mounts an immune response against a protein antigen only if that person inherits an MHC variant that can bind peptides derived from the antigen. For example, a person whose class II molecules bind a peptide from ragweed pollen is genetically prone to allergic reactions against ragweed, whereas an inherited capacity to bind a bacterial peptide may provide resistance to that infection.
- **Disease associations.** Particular HLA alleles are associated with autoimmune and other diseases. The most striking association is between ankylosing spondylitis and **HLA-B27**, with a 100- to 200-fold greater chance of the disease. Most individuals who inherit HLA-B27 never develop it, so the allele is not, by itself, the cause of any disease.

## Define hypersensitivity.

**Hypersensitivity** refers to injurious immune reactions, which are responsible for the pathology associated with immunologic diseases.

Hypersensitivity implies an excessive or harmful reaction to an antigen.


## Classify hypersensitivity.

Hypersensitivity reactions are classified on the basis of the underlying immunologic mechanism.

| Type | Name | Mechanism of injury |
|---|---|---|
| **Type I** | Immediate hypersensitivity | Th2 cells, IgE antibodies, and mast cells and other leucocytes; mast cells release mediators that act on vessels and smooth muscle, and proinflammatory cytokines that recruit inflammatory cells |
| **Type II** | Antibody-mediated disorders | Antibodies, usually IgG and rarely IgM, injure cells by promoting their phagocytosis or lysis, and injure tissues by inducing inflammation; antibodies may also interfere with cellular functions without tissue injury |
| **Type III** | Immune complex-mediated disorders | Antibodies, typically IgG, bind antigens in the circulation or in tissues; the antigen-antibody complexes deposit in tissues and induce inflammation |
| **Type IV** | Cell-mediated immune disorders | T lymphocytes (Th1 and Th17 cells and CD8 positive cytotoxic T lymphocytes) cause the tissue injury |


## Example of each type of hypersensitivity.

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Type I** | Systemic anaphylaxis (for example after penicillin, peanuts, shellfish or bee venom); local reactions such as urticaria, allergic rhinitis (hay fever), asthma, atopic dermatitis and food allergies |
| **Type II** | Autoimmune haemolytic anaemia, thrombocytopenia and agranulocytosis; transfusion reactions; haemolytic disease of the foetus and newborn; some forms of glomerulonephritis; myasthenia gravis; pernicious anaemia; Graves disease [autoimmune hyperthyroidism] |
| **Type III** | Serum sickness (the prototype of systemic immune complex disease); systemic lupus erythematosus; the Arthus reaction [local immune complex disease] |
| **Type IV** | Tuberculin reaction (the classic delayed-type hypersensitivity); contact dermatitis; rheumatoid arthritis; multiple sclerosis; inflammatory bowel disease; type 1 diabetes and viral hepatitis (CD8 positive cytotoxic T lymphocyte injury) |


## Mechanism of type 2 hypersensitivity.

Antibodies, most often IgG, react with antigens present on cell surfaces or in the extracellular matrix and cause disease by destroying these cells, triggering inflammation, or interfering with normal functions.

1. **Opsonisation and phagocytosis.**

- Cells opsonised by IgG antibodies are recognised by phagocyte Fc receptors.
- IgM or IgG antibodies on cell surfaces activate the complement system by the classical pathway; the cleavage product C3b is deposited on the cells and recognised by receptors on macrophages and neutrophils.
- The net result is phagocytosis of the opsonised cells and their destruction inside the phagocytes.
- Complement activation also forms the **membrane attack complex**, which "drills holes" through the lipid bilayer, causing osmotic lysis of the cells.
- In **antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (ADCC)**, cells coated with IgG are killed by NK cells and macrophages that bind through Fc receptors, and cell lysis proceeds without phagocytosis.

2. **Inflammation.**

- When antibodies deposit in fixed tissues, such as basement membranes and extracellular matrix, the resultant injury is due to inflammation.
- The deposited antibodies activate complement, generating chemotactic agents (mainly C5a) and anaphylatoxins (C3a and C5a), which increase vascular permeability.
- Leucocytes are activated by engagement of their C3b and Fc receptors, and release lysosomal enzymes (proteases) and reactive oxygen species that damage the tissues.

3. **Cellular dysfunction.** Antibodies directed against cell surface receptors impair or dysregulate function without causing cell injury or inflammation.

- In myasthenia gravis, antibodies against acetylcholine receptors block neuromuscular transmission.
- In pernicious anaemia, antibodies block intrinsic factor.
- In Graves disease [autoimmune hyperthyroidism], antibodies against the thyroid-stimulating hormone receptor stimulate the thyroid cells, resulting in hyperthyroidism.


## What is anaphylactic reaction?

Systemic **anaphylaxis** is characterised by **vascular shock**, widespread **oedema** and **bronchospasm**.

## Give various examples of anaphylactic reactions.

Systemic anaphylaxis occurs in sensitised individuals.

1. **In hospital**, after administration of:

- foreign proteins, for example antisera
- hormones
- enzymes
- polysaccharides
- drugs, for example the antibiotic **penicillin**

2. **In the community**, after exposure to:

- food allergens, for example **peanuts** and **shellfish**
- insect toxins, for example those in **bee venom**

3. **Extremely small doses** of antigen may trigger it, for example the tiny amounts used in skin testing for allergies.

## What do you mean by autoimmunity?

Immune reactions against self antigens are called **autoimmunity**. Autoimmunity results from the loss of **self tolerance**.

## What do you mean by immunodeficiency?

**Immunodeficiency** diseases are divided into two groups:

- **Primary (congenital) immunodeficiencies**, which are caused by single-gene defects. They are also called inborn errors of immunity.
- **Secondary (acquired) immunodeficiencies**, which may arise as complications of cancers, infections or malnutrition, or as side effects of immunosuppression, irradiation or chemotherapy for cancer, graft rejection and other diseases.

Immunodeficiencies are manifested clinically by **increased infections**, which may be newly acquired or a reactivation of a latent infection.

## Name some autoimmune diseases.

- **Diseases mediated by antibodies:** autoimmune haemolytic anaemia, autoimmune thrombocytopenia, pernicious anaemia, myasthenia gravis, Graves disease [autoimmune hyperthyroidism] and Goodpasture syndrome [anti-glomerular basement membrane disease].
- Diseases mediated by T cells: type 1 diabetes mellitus, rheumatoid arthritis, multiple sclerosis, systemic sclerosis (scleroderma) and Sjögren syndrome [autoimmune dry eyes and dry mouth].
- Systemic disease with a diversity of antibodies: systemic lupus erythematosus.
- Diseases postulated to be autoimmune: inflammatory bowel diseases (Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease] and ulcerative colitis), polyarteritis nodosa, inflammatory myopathies, primary biliary cholangitis and autoimmune (chronic active) hepatitis.

## Write a short note on autoimmune disease.

**Definition.** Immune reactions against self antigens, called autoimmunity, are an important cause of numerous diseases. They are estimated to affect 5 to 8% of the population of the United States, and the incidence is increasing, especially in highly resourced countries. Autoimmunity results from the loss of self tolerance.

**Spectrum.**

- **Organ-specific disease.** In type 1 diabetes the autoreactive T cells and antibodies are specific for β cells of the pancreatic islets. In multiple sclerosis autoreactive T cells react against central nervous system myelin.
- **Systemic disease.** The best example is **systemic lupus erythematosus**, in which a diversity of antibodies directed against DNA, platelets, red cells and protein-phospholipid complexes result in widespread lesions throughout the body. The systemic diseases tend to involve blood vessels and connective tissues and are often called collagen vascular diseases or connective tissue diseases.
- **In between.** In Goodpasture syndrome [anti-glomerular basement membrane disease], antibodies to basement membranes of lung and kidney induce lesions in these organs.

**Self tolerance.**

- **Central tolerance.** Immature self-reactive T cells that recognise self antigens in the thymus are killed by apoptosis (negative selection, or clonal deletion); the protein **AIRE** stimulates expression of peripheral tissue–restricted self antigens in the thymus. Self-reactive B cells in the bone marrow undergo receptor editing, and if this does not occur they undergo apoptosis. Central tolerance is imperfect.
- **Peripheral tolerance.** Self-reactive lymphocytes that escape are silenced by **anergy** (antigen presented without adequate costimulators), **suppression by regulatory T cells** (CD4⁺ cells expressing high levels of CD25 and the transcription factor FOXP3), **deletion by apoptosis**, and sequestration of antigens in immune-privileged sites such as the testis, eye and brain.

**Pathogenesis.** Autoimmunity arises from a combination of the inheritance of susceptibility genes and environmental triggers, such as infections and tissue damage, which promote the activation of self-reactive lymphocytes. The following abnormalities appear to contribute:

- **Defective tolerance or regulation:** clues come from rare inherited (Mendelian) autoimmune disorders, but it is still not known why self tolerance fails in most common autoimmune diseases.
- **Abnormal display of self antigens:** increased expression and persistence of self antigens that are normally cleared, or structural changes that create neoantigens (new epitopes not expressed normally).
- **Inflammation or an initial innate immune response:** microbes or cell injury may elicit local inflammatory reactions that trigger subsequent autoimmunity.

**Susceptibility genes and environmental triggers.**

- **HLA genes** make the greatest contribution among the susceptibility genes; the most striking association is between ankylosing spondylitis and HLA-B27. Many autoimmune diseases are associated with HLA class II alleles.
- **Non-MHC genes** include PTPN22 (rheumatoid arthritis, type 1 diabetes), NOD2 (Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease]) and the IL-2 receptor gene CD25 (type 1 diabetes, multiple sclerosis). Single-gene defects in AIRE, CTLA4, PD1, FAS, FASL and IL2 and its receptor CD25 result in autoimmunity.
- **Infections.** Infections upregulate costimulators on antigen-presenting cells, which may break anergy. Some microbes carry antigens that share amino acid sequences with self antigens (**molecular mimicry**), as in rheumatic heart disease. Viruses such as EBV and HIV cause polyclonal B-cell activation. Tissue injury releases and modifies self antigens, and infections induce cytokines that recruit potentially self-reactive lymphocytes.
- **Other factors.** The gut and skin microbiome may influence the balance of effector and regulatory T cells.

**Clinical features.** The manifestations are extremely varied. Detection of **autoantibodies** is the most widely used clinical test for autoimmune diseases.

## A 30y lady has diffuse thyroid enlargement, exophthalmos, high T3/T4. What immunological mechanisms contribute to its development?

The features are those of **Graves disease** [autoimmune hyperthyroidism], an autoimmune disorder: hyperthyroidism with diffuse enlargement of the gland, and infiltrative ophthalmopathy with exophthalmos. It has a peak incidence between 20 and 50 years of age, and females are affected 5 to 10 times more frequently than males.

1. **Autoantibodies against the TSH receptor.** Graves disease is characterised by the production of autoantibodies against multiple thyroid proteins, most importantly the **TSH receptor**. The most common antibody, **thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulin (TSI)**, is found in about 90% of patients and is almost never seen in other autoimmune diseases of the thyroid.
2. **Antibody-mediated stimulation of cell function.** TSI binds to the TSH receptor and mimics its actions, stimulating adenyl cyclase and increasing the release of thyroid hormones, which explains the high T3 and T4. The thyroid epithelial cells are stimulated, leading to diffuse hypertrophy and hyperplasia and the enlarged gland. This is the converse of antibodies that block receptor function, as in myasthenia gravis.
3. **Genetic susceptibility.** The concordance rate is 30 to 40% in monozygotic twins and less than 5% in dizygotic twins. Susceptibility is associated with polymorphisms in the immune-function genes CTLA4, PTPN22 and IL2RA, and variants in the TSH receptor gene (TSHR).
4. **Exophthalmos.** Activated CD4⁺ helper T cells secrete cytokines that stimulate fibroblast proliferation and synthesis of extracellular matrix proteins (glycosaminoglycans), leading to progressive infiltration of the retro-orbital space. Protrusion of the eyeball is caused by an increase in the volume of retro-orbital connective tissue and extraocular muscles, for four reasons:

- marked infiltration of connective tissue by mononuclear cells, predominantly T cells
- inflammation, oedema and swelling of extraocular muscles
- accumulation of hydrophilic glycosaminoglycans such as hyaluronic acid and chondroitin sulphate
- increased numbers of adipocytes (fatty infiltration)

## What is amyloidosis?

**Amyloidosis** is a generic term for disorders in which proteins with the propensity to aggregate form **fibrils** and deposit in extracellular tissues, causing tissue damage and functional compromise.

These abnormal deposits are produced by the aggregation of proteins that are often misfolded (but are soluble in their normal folded configuration), forming oligomers and then large aggregates that coalesce into insoluble fibrils. The deposits bind a wide variety of proteoglycans and glycosaminoglycans, including heparan sulphate and dermatan sulphate, and plasma proteins, notably **serum amyloid P-component**. The charged sugar groups in these adsorbed proteins give the deposits staining characteristics that were thought to resemble starch (amylose). Therefore the deposits were called amyloid, a name that is firmly entrenched despite the realisation that the deposits are unrelated to starch.

## Define neoplasia / neoplasm.

**Neoplasia** means "new growth", and the collection of cells and stroma composing new growths are referred to as **neoplasms**.

In the modern era, a neoplasm is defined as a disorder of **cell growth** that is triggered by **acquired** or, less commonly, **inherited mutations** affecting a single cell and its **clonal progeny**. These causative mutations alter the function of genes and give the neoplastic cells a growth advantage, resulting in excessive proliferation that is independent of physiologic growth signals and controls.

## Differences between hyperplasia and neoplasia.

| Feature | Hyperplasia | Neoplasia |
|---|---|---|
| **Definition** | Increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue in response to a stimulus | Disorder of cell growth triggered by mutations affecting a single cell and its clonal progeny |
| **Cause** | Action of hormones or growth factors on target cells; in pathologic hyperplasia their excessive or inappropriate action | Acquired or, less commonly, inherited mutations that alter the function of genes |
| **Control of growth** | The process remains controlled | Excessive proliferation that is independent of physiologic growth signals and controls |
| **Effect of removing the stimulus** | Can regress or stabilise if the stimulus is eliminated | Proliferation is independent of physiologic growth signals |
| **Relation to cancer** | Distinct from cancer, but pathologic hyperplasia is a fertile soil in which cancerous proliferations may arise, because increased cell division raises the risk of genetic aberrations | Neoplastic cells have a growth advantage; malignant neoplasms are cancers |
| **Example** | Endometrial hyperplasia, benign prostatic hyperplasia | Benign and malignant tumours |

## Types of neoplasm.

1. **Benign tumours** remain localised at their site of origin and are generally amenable to surgical removal. The suffix "-oma" is attached to the name of the cell type of origin, for example fibroma and chondroma.

- **Adenoma** is applied to benign epithelial neoplasms derived from glandular tissues.
- **Papilloma** produces fingerlike or warty projections from epithelial surfaces.
- **Cystadenoma** forms large cystic masses, such as in the ovary.

2. **Malignant tumours** can invade and destroy adjacent structures and spread to distant sites (metastasise). They are collectively called **cancers**.

- **Sarcomas** arise in solid mesenchymal tissues, for example fibrosarcoma and chondrosarcoma.
- **Leukaemias** arise from blood-forming cells, and **lymphomas** are tumours of lymphocytes or their precursors.
- **Carcinomas** are malignant neoplasms of epithelial cell origin, for example squamous cell carcinoma and adenocarcinoma.

3. **Mixed tumours** show more than one line of differentiation, arising from a single neoplastic clone, for example **pleomorphic adenoma** (mixed tumour of the salivary gland).
4. **Teratoma** contains mature or immature cells or tissues belonging to more than one germ cell layer, sometimes all three, and originates from totipotential germ cells.

## Two examples of each type of neoplasm.

| Type | Benign | Malignant |
|---|---|---|
| **Connective tissue** | Fibroma, lipoma | Fibrosarcoma, liposarcoma |
| **Cartilage and bone** | Chondroma, osteoma | Chondrosarcoma, osteogenic sarcoma |
| **Blood vessels and lymph vessels** | Haemangioma, lymphangioma | Angiosarcoma, lymphangiosarcoma |
| **Muscle** | Leiomyoma, rhabdomyoma | Leiomyosarcoma, rhabdomyosarcoma |
| **Blood cell–forming tissues** | None | Leukaemias, lymphomas |
| **Squamous epithelium** | Squamous cell papilloma | Squamous cell carcinoma |
| **Glandular epithelium** | Adenoma, papilloma | Adenocarcinoma, cystadenocarcinoma |
| **Melanocytes** | Naevus | Melanoma |
| **Kidney and liver** | Renal tubular adenoma, hepatic adenoma | Renal cell carcinoma, hepatocellular carcinoma |
| **Mixed tumours** | Pleomorphic adenoma | Malignant mixed tumour of salivary gland origin, Wilms tumour [nephroblastoma, a childhood renal tumour] |
| **Totipotential cells** | Mature teratoma (dermoid cyst) | Immature teratoma, teratocarcinoma |

## What is cancer?

**Malignant tumours** can invade and destroy adjacent structures and spread to distant sites (metastasise). Malignant tumours are collectively referred to as **cancers**, derived from the Latin word for crab, because they tend to adhere to any tissue that they seize on in an obstinate manner.

## What is carcinoma?

Malignant neoplasms of **epithelial cell origin** are called **carcinomas**.

Carcinomas may be further qualified. In **squamous cell carcinoma** the tumour cells resemble stratified squamous epithelium, whereas in **adenocarcinoma** the neoplastic epithelial cells grow in a glandular pattern. Sometimes the tissue or organ of origin is added as a descriptor, as in renal cell adenocarcinoma or bronchogenic squamous cell carcinoma.

## What is sarcoma?

Malignant tumours arising in solid **mesenchymal tissues** are usually called **sarcomas** (Greek sar, fleshy), for example fibrosarcoma and chondrosarcoma.

## Common examples of carcinoma.

- **Squamous cell carcinoma**, in which the tumour cells resemble stratified squamous epithelium
- **Adenocarcinoma**, in which the neoplastic epithelial cells grow in a glandular pattern
- **Basal cell carcinoma** of the skin
- **Transitional cell carcinoma** of the bladder
- **Bronchogenic carcinoma** of the lung
- **Renal cell carcinoma** of the kidney
- **Hepatocellular carcinoma** of the liver
- **Cystadenocarcinoma** and papillary carcinoma of glandular epithelium, including serous and mucinous cystadenocarcinoma of the ovary
- **Choriocarcinoma** of the placenta
- **Embryonal carcinoma** of the testis

## Common examples of sarcoma.

- **Fibrosarcoma**, from connective tissue
- **Liposarcoma**, from adipose tissue
- **Chondrosarcoma**, from cartilage
- **Osteogenic sarcoma**, from bone
- **Leiomyosarcoma**, from smooth muscle
- **Rhabdomyosarcoma**, from striated muscle
- **Angiosarcoma**, from blood vessels
- **Lymphangiosarcoma**, from lymph vessels

## What is a hamartoma?

**Hamartomas** are disorganised masses composed of cells indigenous to the involved tissue.

Once thought to be a developmental malformation, most hamartomas have clonal chromosomal aberrations that are acquired through somatic mutation and are now considered **benign neoplasms**.

## Write a short note on teratoma.

**Definition.** A **teratoma** is a tumour that contains mature or immature cells or tissues belonging to more than one germ cell layer, and sometimes all three. Most neoplasms are composed of cells from a single germ layer, so teratoma is an exception.

**Origin.** It originates from **totipotential germ cells** that are normally present in the ovary and testis, and sometimes also found in abnormal midline embryonic rests. These cells can differentiate into any cell type found in the body.

**Morphology.**

- The tumour may contain, in a helter-skelter fashion, **bone, epithelium, muscle, fat, nerve** and other tissues.
- A particularly common pattern is the **ovarian cystic teratoma (dermoid cyst)**. It differentiates principally along ectodermal lines to create a cystic tumour lined by squamous epithelium that is replete with **hair, sebaceous glands and teeth**.
- On gross examination, an opened cystic teratoma shows hair, sebaceous material and a tooth. On microscopy there is skin with sebaceous glands, fat cells and a tract of neural tissue.

**Nomenclature.** Mature teratoma (dermoid cyst) is the benign form, and immature teratoma and teratocarcinoma are the malignant forms.

## Define neoplasia with example.

**Neoplasia** means "new growth", and the collection of cells and stroma composing new growths are referred to as **neoplasms**.

In the modern era, a neoplasm is defined as a disorder of **cell growth** that is triggered by **acquired** or, less commonly, **inherited mutations** affecting a single cell and its **clonal progeny**. These causative mutations alter the function of genes and give the neoplastic cells a growth advantage, resulting in excessive proliferation that is independent of physiologic growth signals and controls.

**Examples.** A follicular adenoma of the thyroid and a lipoma are benign neoplasms. An adenocarcinoma of the colon is a malignant neoplasm.

## What are the characteristics of a malignant tumour/cell?

**Characteristics of a malignant tumour.**

1. **Poor differentiation (anaplasia).** Malignant neoplasms show some lack of differentiation, and their structure is often deranged. Highly anaplastic, poorly differentiated tumours are highly predictive of malignant behaviour.
2. **Growth.** Growth is erratic, from slow to rapid, and mitotic figures may be numerous and abnormal. Many rapidly growing cancers develop areas of ischaemic necrosis because the vascular stroma is insufficient.
3. **Local invasion.** Malignant tumours are poorly demarcated from the surrounding normal tissue and lack well-defined cleavage planes. They infiltrate surrounding tissue. Next to metastasis, invasiveness is the most reliable discriminator of malignancy.
4. **Metastasis.** Spread to physically discontinuous sites unequivocally marks a tumour as malignant. It is more likely with large undifferentiated primary tumours.
5. **Function.** Highly anaplastic tumours lose the specialised functions of their tissue of origin, but may acquire unexpected functions, for example the production of foetal proteins or hormones (paraneoplastic syndromes).

**Characteristics of a malignant (anaplastic) cell.**

- **Pleomorphism:** variation in cell size and shape, including tumour giant cells.
- **Abnormal nuclei:** disproportionately large, with a nuclear to cytoplasmic ratio that may approach 1 to 1 (normal 1 to 4 or 1 to 6), irregular shape, coarsely clumped hyperchromatic chromatin and abnormally large nucleoli.
- **Mitoses:** often many, and more important, atypical bizarre mitotic figures.
- **Loss of polarity:** disorganised growth of cells.

## Mention the characteristics of a benign neoplasm.

1. **Well differentiated.** Benign tumours resemble the tissue of origin. The cells of a lipoma may closely resemble normal adipocytes, and only growth into a discrete mass discloses their neoplastic nature.
2. **Mitoses** are usually rare and of normal configuration.
3. **Slow growth.** Growth is usually progressive and slow, and may come to a standstill or regress.
4. **Cohesive, expansile and localised.** They remain at the site of origin and do not invade or infiltrate surrounding normal tissue.
5. **Capsule.** They usually develop a rim of compressed fibrous tissue, which makes the tumour discrete, readily palpable, movable (non-fixed) and easily removed by surgical excision. Exceptions exist, for example haemangiomas, which are often unencapsulated.
6. **No metastasis.**
7. **Retain function.** They often keep the functions of the cells of origin. Well-differentiated tumours of endocrine glands frequently secrete hormones.

## Give the differences between benign and malignant tumour.

| Characteristic | Benign | Malignant |
|---|---|---|
| **Differentiation and anaplasia** | Well differentiated; structure often typical of the tissue of origin | Some lack of differentiation (anaplasia); structure often deranged |
| **Rate of growth** | Usually progressive and slow; may come to a standstill or regress; mitotic figures rare and normal | Erratic, slow to rapid; mitotic figures may be numerous and abnormal |
| **Local invasion** | Usually cohesive, expansile, well-demarcated masses that do not invade or infiltrate surrounding normal tissues | Locally invasive, infiltrating surrounding tissue; sometimes misleadingly cohesive |
| **Metastasis** | Absent | Frequent; more likely with large undifferentiated primary tumours |
| **Capsule** | Circumscribed, with a capsule | Poorly circumscribed, no true capsule |
| **Function** | More likely to retain functions of the cell of origin | May acquire unexpected functions |

## What do you mean by differentiation?

**Differentiation** refers to the extent to which neoplastic parenchymal cells resemble the corresponding normal parenchymal cells, both morphologically and functionally.

## Define anaplasia.

Lack of differentiation is called **anaplasia**.

## What are the features of anaplasia (anaplastic cell)?

Anaplasia is the lack of differentiation. Anaplastic cells show the following:

- **Pleomorphism:** variation in cell size and shape. Cells within the same tumour range from small cells with an undifferentiated appearance to **tumour giant cells** many times larger than their neighbours. Some giant cells have a single huge polymorphic nucleus, and others have two or more large hyperchromatic nuclei.
- **Abnormal nuclear morphology:**
- nuclei disproportionately large, with a nuclear to cytoplasmic ratio that may approach 1 to 1 instead of the normal 1 to 4 or 1 to 6
- nuclear shape variable and often irregular
- chromatin coarsely clumped, distributed along the nuclear membrane, or darker than normal (**hyperchromatic**)
- abnormally large nucleoli
- **Mitoses:** undifferentiated cancers often contain many cells in mitosis, reflecting a high rate of proliferation. More important is the presence of **atypical, bizarre mitotic figures**, for example tripolar spindles.
- **Loss of polarity:** the orientation of anaplastic cells with respect to each other or to supporting structures such as basement membranes is markedly disturbed. Sheets or large masses of tumour cells grow in a disorganised fashion.
- **Loss of specialised function:** highly anaplastic undifferentiated tumours typically lose the specialised functions of their tissue of origin, but sometimes acquire new and unanticipated features, such as the production of foetal proteins or hormones not normally made by their tissue of origin.

## Types of dysplasia.

**Dysplasia** literally means disordered growth. It is encountered principally in epithelial cells and is a premalignant change.

Dysplasia is graded by severity:

- **Mild dysplasia:** mutations associated with full-blown cancer may already be present.
- **Moderately severe dysplasia:** may be completely reversible with removal of the inciting causes.
- **Severe dysplasia:** involves the full thickness of the epithelium, and when the lesion does not penetrate the basement membrane it is called **carcinoma in situ**.

## What is carcinoma in situ (and locally malignant tumour)?

**Carcinoma in situ.** When dysplasia is severe and involves the full thickness of the epithelium but the lesion does not penetrate the basement membrane, it is referred to as carcinoma in situ. It is a cancer that has yet to invade.

- In situ epithelial cancers display all the cytologic features of malignancy.
- Unless treated, they have a high probability of progression to invasive cancer.
- In the cervix, severe epithelial dysplasia or carcinoma in situ frequently antedates the appearance of cancer.
- Carcinoma in situ may persist for years before it becomes invasive.

**Locally malignant tumour.** Some malignant tumours invade early in their course but only rarely metastasise. Malignant neoplasms of the glial cells of the central nervous system, called **gliomas**, and **basal cell carcinomas** of the skin are examples. This shows that the properties of invasiveness and metastasis are separable.

## Two examples of carcinoma in situ.

Carcinoma in situ may be seen in the:

- **skin**
- **breast**
- **bladder**
- **uterine cervix**

## Mention the clinical importance of dysplasia.

1. **Dysplasia is a premalignant change.** Dysplastic changes are often found adjacent to foci of invasive carcinoma. In the cervix, severe epithelial dysplasia or carcinoma in situ frequently antedates the appearance of cancer.
2. **Mutations** associated with full-blown cancer may be present even in "mild" dysplasia.
3. **Progression to cancer is not inevitable.** With removal of the inciting causes, moderately severe dysplasia may be completely reversible.
4. **Severe dysplasia** involving the full thickness of the epithelium without breaching the basement membrane is carcinoma in situ, which unless treated has a high probability of progression to invasive cancer. It may persist for years before it becomes invasive.
5. **Detection.** The Pap smear [cervical cytology screening] enables detection of precursor lesions and early curable cancers of the cervix.

## Write down the features of dysplasia.

**Dysplasia** literally means "disordered growth". It is encountered principally in epithelial cells.

- **Pleomorphism** of the cells.
- **Large hyperchromatic nuclei** with a high nuclear to cytoplasmic ratio.
- **Architectural disarray** of the epithelial surface, with loss of orderly differentiation. In dysplastic squamous epithelium the normal maturation of tall basal cells to flattened squames fails in part or entirely, and the epithelium is replaced by basal-like cells with hyperchromatic nuclei.
- **Increased mitotic figures**, seen throughout the dysplastic epithelium rather than only in the basal layer.

## How does dysplasia differ from anaplasia?

| Feature | Dysplasia | Anaplasia |
|---|---|---|
| **Meaning** | Disordered growth | Lack of differentiation |
| **Where found** | Principally epithelial cells | Malignant tumour cells |
| **Nature** | A premalignant change | A feature highly predictive of malignant behaviour |
| **Cell changes** | Pleomorphism, large hyperchromatic nuclei, high nuclear to cytoplasmic ratio, architectural disarray, increased mitoses | Pleomorphism, abnormal nuclei, abnormal mitoses, loss of polarity, tumour giant cells |
| **Reversibility** | Moderately severe dysplasia may be completely reversible | Not reversible |
| **Invasion** | Basement membrane intact (carcinoma in situ when full thickness) | Associated with invasion and metastasis |

## Define metastasis (short note).

**Metastasis** is defined as the spread of a primary tumour to physically discontinuous sites, an event that unequivocally marks a tumour as **malignant**.

- The invasiveness of cancers permits them to penetrate blood vessels, lymphatics and body cavities, providing the opportunity for spread.
- All malignant tumours can metastasise, but some do so very infrequently, for example gliomas and basal cell carcinomas.
- Leukaemias and lymphomas are derived from cells that normally enter the bloodstream, so they are often disseminated at diagnosis and are always considered malignant.
- About 30% of solid tumours (excluding skin cancers other than melanoma) present as metastatic disease.
- The likelihood of metastasis correlates with lack of differentiation, aggressive local invasion, rapid growth and large size, but there are innumerable exceptions.
- Metastatic spread strongly reduces the possibility of cure.

## Mention the routes/pathways of spread of a malignant tumour.

Dissemination of cancers occurs through three pathways:

1. **Seeding of body cavities and surfaces.** This occurs when malignant neoplasms spread to a natural open field lacking physical barriers.

- Most often involved is the **peritoneal cavity**, but any body cavity and associated surface, such as the pleura, pericardium, meninges and joints, may be affected.
- It is particularly characteristic of **ovarian carcinoma**. Mucus-secreting appendiceal or ovarian carcinomas may fill the peritoneal cavity with a gelatinous mass called pseudomyxoma peritonei.

2. **Lymphatic spread.** This is the most common pathway for the initial dissemination of **carcinomas**; sarcomas also sometimes use it.

- The pattern follows the natural routes of lymphatic drainage. Breast carcinomas usually spread first to the axillary lymph nodes, then to infraclavicular and supraclavicular nodes. Carcinomas of the lung spread first to perihilar tracheobronchial and mediastinal nodes.
- Local nodes are sometimes bypassed, which is called skip metastasis.
- A **sentinel lymph node** is the first node in a regional lymphatic basin that receives lymph flow from the primary tumour.
- Enlarged lymph nodes do not always harbour metastases.

3. **Haematogenous spread.** This is typical of **sarcomas** but is also seen with carcinomas.

- Involved vessels are usually small veins, because arteries, with their thicker walls, are more resistant to penetration.
- Tumour cells often come to rest in the first capillary bed they encounter.

## Common organs where metastasis occurs.

- The **liver** and the **lungs** are most commonly affected by haematogenous metastases, because their capillary beds act as filters for blood supplied by the portal vein and by the venae cavae.
- **Vertebrae** are affected by cancers arising near the vertebral column, such as carcinomas of the thyroid and prostate, which embolise through the paravertebral plexus.
- Other tumours show organ tropism:
- carcinoma of the prostate and breast spreads preferentially to **bone** and lung
- lung carcinoma tends to involve the **adrenals** and the **brain**
- neuroblastoma spreads to the liver and bones
- melanoma of the eye tends to spread to the liver
- Colon carcinomas are far more likely to give rise to metastases in the liver, the first organ downstream of the tumour.

## Carcinomas that prefer haematogenous spread.

- **Renal cell carcinoma** often invades the branches of the renal vein and then the renal vein itself, growing in a snakelike fashion up the inferior vena cava until it sometimes reaches the right side of the heart.
- **Hepatocellular carcinoma** often penetrates portal and hepatic radicles and grows into the main venous channels. Such intravenous growth may not be accompanied by widespread metastasis.
- **Carcinomas of the thyroid and prostate** often embolise through the paravertebral plexus, producing vertebral metastases.

## What is invasion?

The growth of cancers is accompanied by progressive **invasion**, destruction of surrounding tissue and eventually systemic spread. Malignant tumours are, in general, poorly demarcated from the surrounding normal tissue and lack well-defined cleavage planes; most do not recognise normal anatomic boundaries.

Next to metastasis, invasiveness is the most reliable discriminator of malignant and benign tumours. Given time, malignant tumours penetrate the wall of the colon or uterus, for example, or fungate through the surface of the skin. Local invasion may damage or destroy vital structures and is a prerequisite for distant spread.

## Steps of invasion.

Invasion of the extracellular matrix (ECM) initiates the metastatic cascade. Carcinoma cells must breach the underlying basement membrane, traverse the interstitial connective tissue and penetrate the vascular basement membrane. It occurs in four steps:

1. **Loosening of tumour cell to tumour cell interactions.** Cancer cells dissociate from one another because of altered adhesion molecules. **E-cadherin** function is lost by mutation or silenced, often through epithelial to mesenchymal transition (EMT), which is controlled by the transcription factors SNAIL and TWIST.
2. **Degradation of the ECM.** Tumour cells, or stromal cells induced by them, secrete proteolytic enzymes such as **matrix metalloproteinases (MMPs)**, cathepsin D and urokinase plasminogen activator, which digest the basement membrane and interstitial connective tissue. Concentrations of metalloproteinase inhibitors are also reduced.
3. **Attachment to remodelled ECM components.** Tumour cells change the expression of **integrins**. They resist anoikis (apoptosis due to loss of adhesion), and cleavage of collagen IV and laminin generates new binding sites that stimulate migration.
4. **Migration and invasion of tumour cells.** Locomotion propels tumour cells through the degraded basement membranes, stimulated by tumour cell–derived cytokines (autocrine motility factors), cleavage products of matrix components, and stromal cell–derived factors such as hepatocyte growth factor, which binds the receptor MET.

## How does a malignant tumour spread through the hematogenous route?

**Haematogenous spread** is typical of **sarcomas** but is also seen with carcinomas.

- Histologic evidence of penetration of small vessels at the site of the primary tumour is an ominous feature. The involved vessels are usually small veins, because arteries, with thicker walls, are more resistant to penetration.
- Arterial spread may occur when tumour cells pass through pulmonary capillary beds or pulmonary arteriovenous shunts, or when cancers in the lung give rise to tumour emboli.
- With venous invasion, tumour cells often come to rest in the first capillary bed they encounter. Metastases therefore most commonly affect the **liver** and **lungs**, because their capillary beds filter blood from the portal vein and from the vena cavae.
- Cancers near the vertebral column, such as carcinomas of the thyroid and prostate, embolise through the paravertebral plexus and produce vertebral metastases.
- Some cancers grow within large veins: **renal cell carcinoma** grows up the renal vein and inferior vena cava, and **hepatocellular carcinoma** grows into portal and hepatic veins.
- Patterns of spread are often cancer specific, and cannot be wholly explained by the site of the primary tumour and its venous drainage.

## Describe in short the mechanism of invasion and metastasis.

The metastatic cascade has two phases: **invasion of the extracellular matrix (ECM)**, and **vascular dissemination, tissue homing and colonisation**.

**1. Invasion of the ECM** occurs in four steps.

1. Loosening of tumour cell to tumour cell contacts, through loss of **E-cadherin**.
2. Degradation of the ECM by proteolytic enzymes such as **matrix metalloproteinases** and cathepsins.
3. Attachment to novel ECM components.
4. Migration of tumour cells.

**2. Vascular dissemination, homing and colonisation.**

- Tumour cells in the circulation are vulnerable to shear stress, anoikis and immune defences. Those that establish metastases are more likely to travel as multicellular aggregates, clumped with platelets.
- Where cells arrest depends on the location and vascular drainage of the primary tumour, the tropism of tumour cells for specific tissues (adhesion molecules, chemokine receptors, the seed-soil hypothesis), and escape from tumour dormancy.
- At a distant site, tumour cells extravasate by transmigration between endothelial cells and egress through the basement membrane. They then colonise by secreting factors that make the stroma habitable.

## Fundamental cellular changes considered hallmarks of cancer.

All cancers display eight fundamental changes in cell physiology, which are the **hallmarks of cancer**:

1. **Self-sufficiency in growth signals.** Tumours can proliferate without external stimuli, usually because of oncogene activation.
2. **Insensitivity to growth-inhibitory signals.** Tumours may not respond to molecules that inhibit proliferation of normal cells, typically because of inactivation of tumour suppressor genes.
3. **Altered cellular metabolism.** Tumour cells switch to aerobic glycolysis, called the Warburg effect [aerobic glycolysis in cancer cells], which enables synthesis of the macromolecules and organelles needed for rapid growth.
4. **Evasion of apoptosis.** Tumours are resistant to programmed cell death.
5. **Limitless replicative potential (immortality).** Tumours have unrestricted proliferative capacity, a stem cell–like property that avoids cellular senescence and mitotic catastrophe.
6. **Sustained angiogenesis.** Tumours cannot grow without a vascular supply, so they must induce angiogenesis.
7. Ability to invade and metastasise.
8. Ability to evade the host immune response.

Two **enabling characteristics** accelerate the acquisition of these changes: genomic instability (mutator phenotype) and tumour-promoting (cancer-enabling) inflammation.

## Four principal classes of genes that cause cancer after mutation.

1. **Growth-promoting proto-oncogenes.** Activating mutations cause a gain of function, so they can transform cells despite a normal copy of the same gene; oncogenes are dominant over their normal counterparts.
2. **Growth-inhibiting tumour suppressor genes.** Mutations generally cause a loss of function, and in most instances both alleles must be damaged before transformation occurs.
3. Genes that regulate programmed cell death (apoptosis).
4. Genes responsible for DNA repair.

## Examples of oncogenes and tumour suppressor genes.

**Oncogenes:**

| Oncogene | Mode of activation | Associated tumour |
|---|---|---|
| ERBB1 (EGFR) | Mutation | Adenocarcinoma of lung |
| ERBB2 (HER2) | Amplification | Breast carcinoma |
| KRAS | Point mutation | Colon, lung and pancreatic tumours |
| BRAF | Point mutation | Melanomas, leukaemias, colon carcinoma |
| ABL | Translocation (BCR-ABL) | Chronic myeloid leukaemia, acute lymphoblastic leukaemia |
| MYC | Translocation | Burkitt lymphoma [high-grade B-cell lymphoma with MYC translocation] |
| NMYC | Amplification | Neuroblastoma |
| CDK4 | Amplification or point mutation | Glioblastoma, melanoma, sarcoma |
| Cyclin D1 (CCND1) | Translocation or amplification | Mantle cell lymphoma, breast and oesophageal cancers |

**Tumour suppressor genes and the familial syndromes they cause:**

| Tumour suppressor gene | Familial syndrome |
|---|---|
| RB | Familial retinoblastoma |
| TP53 | Li-Fraumeni syndrome [inherited TP53 mutation cancer syndrome] |
| APC | Familial adenomatous polyposis |
| NF1 | Neurofibromatosis type 1 |
| PTEN | Cowden syndrome [PTEN hamartoma tumour syndrome] |
| CDKN2A | Familial melanoma |
| VHL | von Hippel-Lindau syndrome [inherited VHL tumour syndrome] |
| STK11 | Peutz-Jeghers syndrome [hamartomatous gastrointestinal polyps with mucocutaneous pigmentation] |
| CDH1 (E-cadherin) | Familial gastric cancer |
| BRCA1 and BRCA2 | Familial breast and ovarian cancer |
| WT1 | Familial Wilms tumour [nephroblastoma, a childhood renal tumour] |

## What is Li-Fraumeni syndrome?

**Li-Fraumeni syndrome** [inherited TP53 mutation cancer syndrome] occurs in individuals who inherit one mutated copy of the tumour suppressor gene **TP53** (on chromosome 17p13.1).

- Only one additional hit in the remaining normal allele is needed to abrogate TP53 function.
- They have a **25-fold** greater chance of developing a malignant tumour by age 50 than the general population.
- The spectrum of tumours is broad. The most common are **sarcomas, breast cancers, leukaemias, brain tumours and carcinomas of the adrenal cortex**.
- Affected persons often develop cancer at younger ages and are more likely to have multiple primary tumours of varying types.

## Which factor induces angiogenesis?

**Hypoxia** stabilises HIF1α, an oxygen-sensitive transcription factor that activates transcription of the pro-angiogenic cytokines vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) and basic fibroblast growth factor (bFGF).

Loss of p53, and gain-of-function mutations in RAS or MYC, also increase VEGF production.

## How does angiogenesis affect tumour growth?

- A solid tumour cannot enlarge beyond **1 to 2 mm** in diameter unless it induces angiogenesis, because oxygen, nutrients and waste can diffuse only across that distance from existing vessels.
- Early in their development most human tumours do not induce angiogenesis, so they remain small or in situ, possibly for years, until an **angiogenic switch** ends the quiescent stage.
- New vessels supply nutrients and oxygen, and proliferating endothelial cells also stimulate growth of adjacent tumour cells by secreting growth factors such as insulin-like growth factors and PDGF.
- Tumour vessels are leaky and dilated, with a haphazard pattern of connection.
- Angiogenesis gives tumour cells ready access to these abnormal vessels, so it also contributes to **metastasis**.
- Angiogenesis is thus an essential requirement for malignancy. VEGF inhibitors such as bevacizumab prolong the clinical course of several advanced cancers but are not curative.

## Cells involved in the anti-tumour immune mechanism.

- **CD8⁺ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs)** are the principal mechanism of tumour eradication. They recognise tumour antigens presented by MHC class I molecules and kill tumour cells directly and serially.
- **Dendritic cells** ingest tumour cells or released tumour antigens, migrate to draining lymph nodes and present the antigens to CD8⁺ T cells.
- **Natural killer (NK) cells**, after activation with IL-2 and IL-15, can lyse a wide range of tumour cells, including those that have lost MHC class I expression, without prior sensitisation. They may constitute a first line of defence.
- **CD4⁺ T cells**, especially Th1 cells, in tumour infiltrates are associated with a better prognosis in some cancers, such as colorectal carcinoma.
- **Macrophages** activated by interferon-γ, which is secreted by T cells and NK cells, may kill tumour cells.

## What are the carcinogenic agents?

1. **Chemical carcinogens**, which are direct-acting or indirect-acting.
2. **Radiation**, as ultraviolet rays of sunlight and as ionising electromagnetic and particulate radiation.
3. **Microbial agents**, which are oncogenic RNA and DNA viruses and the bacterium Helicobacter pylori.

## Types of chemical carcinogens.

1. **Direct-acting carcinogens** do not require metabolic conversion to become carcinogenic. Most are weak carcinogens, but some are important because they are used as cancer chemotherapy (alkylating agents such as cyclophosphamide, chlorambucil and nitrosoureas), which may evoke a second cancer, usually acute myeloid leukaemia.
2. **Indirect-acting carcinogens (procarcinogens)** require metabolic conversion to become active carcinogens, which are called ultimate carcinogens. Most chemical carcinogens act indirectly. They are metabolised by cytochrome P-450–dependent monooxygenases. Examples:

- **polycyclic hydrocarbons**, such as benzo[a]pyrene in soot and tobacco smoke, also produced by broiling or grilling meats and present in smoked meats and fish
- **aromatic amines and azo dyes**
- **aflatoxin B₁**, produced by the mould Aspergillus, which is linked to hepatocellular carcinoma
- nitrosamines, formed from nitrites used as food preservatives, which are suspected carcinogens

3. **Tumour promoters** are not tumourigenic by themselves. They stimulate proliferation of initiated cells, for example oestrogen and tissue repair.

## Steps of carcinogenesis.

Carcinogenesis is a **multistep process** resulting from the accumulation of complementary mutations in a stepwise fashion over time.

1. **Initiation.** Exposure of a cell to a sufficient dose of a carcinogenic agent causes permanent DNA damage (mutation). The first driver mutation that starts a cell on the path to malignancy is the initiating mutation.
2. **Promotion.** Promoters induce proliferation and clonal expansion of the initiated cells, producing a preneoplastic clone.
3. **Progression.** Driven to proliferate, subclones of the initiated cells acquire additional driver mutations, and eventually a cancerous clone with all the hallmarks of cancer emerges. Mutations that destabilise the genome greatly increase the number of mutations.

In colon carcinoma, adenomas progressively enlarge and undergo malignant transformation. Certain mutations occur early, such as mutations in the tumour suppressor gene **APC**, and others late, such as mutations in **TP53**.

By the time a tumour comes to clinical attention it has a mass of about 1 g, or about 10⁹ cells, and has gone through a minimum of 30 cell doublings.

## What do you mean by initiation?

**Initiation** results from exposure of cells to a sufficient dose of a carcinogenic agent. It causes **permanent DNA damage (mutations)**.

Initiating chemical carcinogens are highly reactive electrophiles that inflict nonlethal damage to DNA, which is repaired in some error-prone fashion, and the mutated cell passes the DNA lesions to its daughter cells. Initiation alone is not enough for a tumour to develop.

## What do you mean by promotion?

**Promoters** can induce tumours to arise from initiated cells, but they are not tumourigenic by themselves. Application of promoters leads to proliferation and **clonal expansion** of initiated (mutated) cells, producing a preneoplastic clone.

Further proliferation induces additional mutations and the emergence of a malignant tumour. Factors that stimulate proliferation act as tumour promoters, for example unopposed oestrogenic stimulation of the endometrium and breast, and chronic inflammatory processes associated with tissue repair and fibrosis.

## Examples of chronic inflammation that cause risk of cancer.

| Chronic inflammatory condition | Associated neoplasm |
|---|---|
| **Asbestosis, silicosis** | Mesothelioma, lung carcinoma |
| **Inflammatory bowel disease** | Colorectal carcinoma |
| **Lichen sclerosus** | Vulval squamous cell carcinoma |
| **Pancreatitis** | Pancreatic carcinoma |
| **Chronic cholecystitis** | Gallbladder carcinoma |
| **Reflux oesophagitis, Barrett oesophagus** [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] | Oesophageal carcinoma |
| **Sjögren syndrome [autoimmune dry eyes and dry mouth], Hashimoto thyroiditis** [autoimmune thyroiditis causing hypothyroidism] | Extranodal marginal zone (MALT) lymphoma |
| **Opisthorchis (liver fluke) cholangitis** | Cholangiocarcinoma, colon carcinoma |
| **Gastritis and ulcers (Helicobacter pylori)** | Gastric adenocarcinoma, MALT lymphoma |
| Hepatitis (hepatitis B and C viruses) | Hepatocellular carcinoma |
| **Osteomyelitis** | Carcinoma in draining sinuses |
| **Chronic cervicitis (human papillomavirus)** | Cervical carcinoma |
| **Chronic cystitis (schistosomiasis)** | Bladder carcinoma |

## Oncogenic infective agents (viruses/bacteria) and the cancers they produce.

| Agent | Cancer |
|---|---|
| **HTLV-1** (RNA retrovirus) | Adult T-cell leukaemia/lymphoma |
| **Human papillomavirus** types 16 and 18 | Squamous cell carcinomas of the cervix, anogenital region and head and neck (oropharynx) |
| **Epstein-Barr virus** [herpesvirus that causes infectious mononucleosis] | Burkitt lymphoma [high-grade B-cell lymphoma with MYC translocation], B-cell lymphomas in immunosuppressed patients, nasopharyngeal carcinoma, Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] |
| Hepatitis B and C viruses | Hepatocellular carcinoma (70 to 85% worldwide) |
| **Human herpesvirus 8** | Kaposi sarcoma [HHV-8-associated vascular tumour] |
| **Merkel cell [neuroendocrine touch-receptor cell of the epidermis] polyomavirus** | Merkel cell carcinoma |
| **Helicobacter pylori** | Gastric adenocarcinoma, gastric B-cell lymphoma (MALToma) |

## What do you mean by oncogene?

**Oncogenes** are mutated genes that cause excessive cell growth, even in the absence of growth factors and other growth-promoting external cues.

- Oncogenes are mutated or overexpressed versions of normal cellular genes, called **proto-oncogenes**.
- Proto-oncogenes may encode growth factors, growth factor receptors, signal transducers, transcription factors or cell cycle components.
- Oncogenes usually encode **oncoproteins** with functions like those of the normal counterparts, but they are **constitutively active**, so cells are freed from normal checkpoints and proliferate excessively.
- Mutations are gain of function, so one mutant allele is enough and oncogenes are dominant.

| Oncogene | Mode of activation | Associated tumour |
|---|---|---|
| ERBB1 (EGFR) | Mutation | Adenocarcinoma of lung |
| ERBB2 (HER2) | Amplification | Breast carcinoma |
| KRAS | Point mutation | Colon, lung and pancreatic tumours |
| BRAF | Point mutation | Melanomas, leukaemias, colon carcinoma |
| ABL | Translocation (BCR-ABL) | Chronic myeloid leukaemia, acute lymphoblastic leukaemia |
| MYC | Translocation | Burkitt lymphoma [high-grade B-cell lymphoma with MYC translocation] |
| NMYC | Amplification | Neuroblastoma |

## How does a cell become malignant?

Carcinogenesis is a **multistep process** resulting from the accumulation of complementary mutations in a stepwise fashion over time.

1. **Initiation.** A cell exposed to a sufficient dose of a carcinogenic agent suffers permanent DNA damage (mutation). The first **driver mutation** that starts a cell on the path to malignancy is the initiating mutation.
2. **Promotion.** Promoters induce proliferation and clonal expansion of the initiated cell, producing a preneoplastic clone.
3. **Progression.** Subclones acquire additional driver mutations, and eventually a cancerous clone with all the hallmarks of cancer emerges.

- No single mutation is fully transforming, so the initiated cell must acquire further driver mutations.
- Loss-of-function mutations in genes that maintain genomic integrity are a common early step. They increase the number of **passenger mutations**, which are much more common than driver mutations.
- The mutations affect four classes of genes: growth-promoting proto-oncogenes, growth-inhibiting tumour suppressor genes, genes that regulate apoptosis, and DNA repair genes.
- In colon carcinoma, adenomas progressively enlarge and undergo malignant transformation. **APC** mutations occur early and **TP53** mutations late.

## Write about the role of genes in tumourigenesis.

Four principal classes of genes are the targets of cancer-causing mutations.

1. **Growth-promoting proto-oncogenes.** Activating mutations cause a gain of function, so they can transform cells despite a normal copy of the same gene. Oncogenes are dominant over their normal counterparts.
2. **Growth-inhibiting tumour suppressor genes.** Mutations generally cause a loss of function, and in most instances both alleles must be damaged before transformation occurs.
3. **Genes that regulate programmed cell death (apoptosis).**
4. **Genes responsible for DNA repair.** Their loss lets mutations accumulate in other genes (the mutator phenotype).

Together these changes produce the hallmarks of cancer, including self-sufficiency in growth signals, insensitivity to growth inhibition and evasion of apoptosis.

## Write a short note on tumour suppressor genes.

**Tumour suppressor genes** act as negative regulators of cell proliferation, and loss of their function therefore leads to excessive growth. A more inclusive definition is a protein or gene that opposes any of the hallmarks of cancer.

- Their products control checkpoints that prevent uncontrolled growth. RB and p53 form part of a network that recognises DNA-damaging (genotoxic) stress and shuts down proliferation. p53 may also induce apoptosis.
- Some are involved in cell differentiation, and others alter cell metabolism or ensure genomic stability.
- Their products may be transcription factors, cell cycle inhibitors, signal transduction molecules, cell surface receptors or regulators of the response to DNA damage.

**RB, the prototype.** Familial retinoblastoma led to the discovery of RB, the first tumour suppressor gene.

- Both alleles must be lost to cause retinoblastoma (the **two-hit hypothesis**).
- In familial cases a child inherits one defective copy (first hit), and a somatic mutation destroys the other copy (second hit). The risk is inherited as an autosomal dominant trait, but at the cell level the loss of function is recessive.
- In sporadic cases both alleles are lost by two somatic mutations.
- Carriers also have a greatly increased risk of osteosarcoma and other soft tissue sarcomas.

| Gene | Familial syndrome | Sporadic cancers |
|---|---|---|
| APC | Familial colonic polyps and carcinomas | Carcinomas of stomach, colon, pancreas; melanoma |
| NF1 | Neurofibromatosis type 1 | Neuroblastoma, juvenile myeloid leukaemia |
| RB | Familial retinoblastoma | Retinoblastoma, osteosarcoma, carcinomas of breast and colon |
| TP53 | Li-Fraumeni syndrome [inherited TP53 mutation cancer syndrome] | Most human cancers |
| BRCA1, BRCA2 | Familial breast and ovarian carcinoma | Rare |
| VHL | Von Hippel-Lindau syndrome [inherited VHL tumour syndrome] | Renal cell carcinoma |
| WT1 | Familial Wilms tumour [nephroblastoma, a childhood renal tumour] | Wilms tumour |

## Briefly state the role of environmental factors in cancer development.

Environmental influences are the **dominant risk factors for most cancers**. Evidence includes the tight tracking of lung cancer incidence with smoking habits, the fall in stomach cancer in the 20th century, and wide geographic variation in the incidence of specific cancers.

1. **Infectious agents.** About 15% of all cancers worldwide are caused directly or indirectly by infectious agents, for example HPV in cervical carcinoma.
2. **Smoking.** Implicated in cancers of the mouth, pharynx, larynx, oesophagus, pancreas and bladder, and in about 90% of lung cancers.
3. **Alcohol.** Increases the risk of carcinoma of the oropharynx, larynx and oesophagus, and of hepatocellular carcinoma through cirrhosis. The risk rises synergistically with smoking.
4. **Diet.** Differences in diet are ascribed for the wide geographic variation in colorectal, prostate and breast carcinoma.
5. **Obesity.** Associated with an increased risk of diverse cancers, for example endometrial cancer.
6. **Reproductive history.** Lifelong exposure to oestrogen, especially unopposed by progesterone, raises the risk of breast and endometrial cancer.
7. **Carcinogens and tumour promoters.** These include ultraviolet rays, air pollutants, arsenic in well water (particularly in Bangladesh), asbestos at work, and certain medications such as methotrexate.

## Give in short what you know about viral oncogenesis (oncogenic virus).

About 15% of all cancers worldwide are caused directly or indirectly by infectious agents. Oncogenic viruses are of two kinds.

**Oncogenic RNA virus.** Only one human retrovirus, **HTLV-1**, is firmly implicated in cancer. It causes **adult T-cell leukaemia/lymphoma**, endemic in parts of Japan, the Caribbean basin, South America and Africa.

- HTLV-1 has a tropism for CD4⁺ T cells, which are the target for neoplastic transformation.
- It contains no recognisable oncogene, but its genome has the genes **tax** and **HBZ**, whose products alter transcription of host genes and interact with host signalling proteins.
- Proviral integration sites are clonal, so infection occurred before transformation.

**Oncogenic DNA viruses.** Five human DNA viruses are implicated: HPV, EBV, HBV, Merkel cell [neuroendocrine touch-receptor cell of the epidermis] polyomavirus and human herpesvirus 8 (Kaposi sarcoma [HHV-8-associated vascular tumour] herpesvirus). The hepatitis C virus, although not a DNA virus, is also associated with cancer.

- **HPV.** High-risk types 16 and 18 cause squamous cell carcinomas of the cervix, anogenital region and head and neck. Types 1, 2, 4 and 7 cause benign warts, and types 6 and 11 genital warts of low malignant potential.

## Name the oncogenic viruses/microbes with associated cancers.

| Agent | Cancer |
|---|---|
| **HTLV-1** (RNA retrovirus) | Adult T-cell leukaemia/lymphoma |
| **Human papillomavirus** types 16 and 18 | Squamous cell carcinomas of the cervix, anogenital region and head and neck (oropharynx) |
| **Epstein-Barr virus** [herpesvirus that causes infectious mononucleosis] | Burkitt lymphoma [high-grade B-cell lymphoma with MYC translocation], B-cell lymphomas in immunosuppressed patients, nasopharyngeal carcinoma, Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] |
| Hepatitis B and C viruses | Hepatocellular carcinoma (70 to 85% worldwide) |
| **Human herpesvirus 8** | Kaposi sarcoma [HHV-8-associated vascular tumour] |
| **Merkel cell [neuroendocrine touch-receptor cell of the epidermis] polyomavirus** | Merkel cell carcinoma |
| **Helicobacter pylori** | Gastric adenocarcinoma, gastric B-cell lymphoma (MALToma) |

## Write a short note on tumour angiogenesis.

- A solid tumour cannot enlarge beyond **1 to 2 mm** in diameter unless it induces **angiogenesis**, because oxygen, nutrients and waste can diffuse only across that distance from existing vessels.
- Early in their development most human tumours do not induce angiogenesis, so they stay small or in situ, possibly for years, until an **angiogenic switch** ends the quiescent stage.
- **Hypoxia** stabilises **HIF1α**, which activates transcription of **VEGF** and **basic fibroblast growth factor (bFGF)**. Loss of p53 and gain-of-function mutations in RAS or MYC also increase VEGF production.
- New vessels supply nutrients and oxygen, and proliferating endothelial cells also stimulate growth of adjacent tumour cells by secreting growth factors such as insulin-like growth factors and PDGF.
- Tumour vessels are leaky and dilated, with a haphazard pattern of connection. They give tumour cells ready access to the circulation, so angiogenesis also contributes to **metastasis**.
- Angiogenesis is therefore an essential requirement for malignancy. VEGF inhibitors such as bevacizumab prolong the clinical course of several advanced cancers but are not curative.

## Write a short note on tumour heterogeneity.

Cancers are **clonal** in origin: early on, all the cells are genetically identical, being the progeny of a single founding transformed cell. By the time the tumour comes to clinical attention, however, its cells are often extremely **heterogeneous genetically**.

- A tumour reaches about 1 g (about 10⁹ cells) after at least 30 cell doublings. During this expansion individual cells acquire additional mutations at random, especially in tumours with a mutator phenotype.
- These diverse **subclones** compete for nutrients and microenvironmental niches, and the fittest subclones (Darwinian selection) dominate the tumour mass.
- The tendency of tumours to become more aggressive over time is **tumour progression**.
- Sequencing of several regions of renal cell carcinomas and their metastases found mutations common to all sites (present in the founding cell) and mutations unique to a subset of sites (acquired later). The latter can be used to build tumour "family trees".
- Subclones may arise and diverge in years, months or even weeks.
- **Therapy** is a strong selective pressure: tumours that recur after therapy are almost always resistant to the same treatment, because therapy selects for resistant subclones.
- Cancers also show **epigenetic heterogeneity** from cell to cell, which may cause drug resistance.

## What is a precancerous condition/lesion?

**Precursor (precancerous) lesions** are localised morphologic changes that identify a field of epithelium at increased risk of malignant transformation. These changes may take the form of hyperplasia, metaplasia or dysplasia.

1. **Dysplasia and carcinoma in situ.** Dysplasia is a premalignant change, and severe dysplasia that does not breach the basement membrane is carcinoma in situ. Progression to cancer is not inevitable.
2. **Hyperplasia**, often from chronic exposure to trophic factors, for example endometrial hyperplasia caused by sustained oestrogenic stimulation.
3. **Benign neoplasms.** A classic example is the colonic villous adenoma, which progresses to cancer in about 50% of cases if left untreated. Most benign tumours transform rarely (uterine leiomyomas, pleomorphic adenoma) and others not at all (lipomas).
4. **Metaplasia**, for example Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus]. Not all metaplastic epithelium becomes dysplastic.

**Chronic inflammatory conditions** also predispose to cancer, for example inflammatory bowel disease (colorectal carcinoma), chronic hepatitis (hepatocellular carcinoma), reflux oesophagitis and Barrett oesophagus (oesophageal carcinoma), and chronic cervicitis from HPV (cervical carcinoma).

## Clinical features of tumour.

Any tumour, even a benign one, may cause morbidity and mortality.

1. **Local effects.** Location is a critical determinant of the clinical effects.

- Tumours may impinge on vital tissues and impair their function, cause death of involved tissues, and provide a nidus for infection.
- A small pituitary adenoma can compress and destroy the surrounding gland and lead to hypopituitarism. Cancers within or metastatic to an endocrine gland may cause endocrine insufficiency.
- Neoplasms in the gut may cause obstruction and sometimes intussusception.
- Erosive growth or pressure on skin or mucosa may cause ulceration, secondary infection and bleeding, for example melaena and haematuria in neoplasms of the gut and urinary tract.

2. **Hormonal effects.** Neoplasms of endocrine glands may produce hormones. This is more typical of benign tumours. For example, a benign β-cell adenoma of the pancreatic islets less than 1 cm in diameter may produce enough insulin to cause fatal hypoglycaemia.
3. **Cancer cachexia.** A hypercatabolic state with loss of muscle mass (with or without loss of fat) that diet alone does not explain.

- It occurs in about **50%** of cancer patients, most often in advanced gastrointestinal, pancreatic and lung cancers, and accounts for about **30%** of cancer deaths.
- It is marked by extreme weight loss, fatigue, muscle atrophy, anaemia, anorexia and oedema.
- Mediators include **TNF, IL-1 and IL-6**, which drive muscle proteolysis, and **GDF-15**, which suppresses appetite.

4. **Paraneoplastic syndromes.** Symptom complexes in patients with cancer that tumour spread, or hormones normally made by the tissue of origin, do not explain.

- They occur in about **10%** of patients with cancer.
- They may be the earliest sign of an occult tumour, may be lethal, and may mimic metastatic disease.
- Examples include Cushing syndrome [glucocorticoid excess] from ectopic ACTH in small cell carcinoma of the lung, hypercalcaemia from parathyroid hormone-related protein, hypoglycaemia, polycythaemia from erythropoietin, and venous thrombosis (Trousseau phenomenon [migratory thrombophlebitis associated with cancer]).

## What is cancer cachexia?

**Cancer cachexia** is a hypercatabolic state defined by a loss of muscle mass (with or without loss of fat) that cannot be explained by diminished food intake.

- It occurs in about **50%** of cancer patients, most commonly in advanced gastrointestinal, pancreatic and lung cancers, and is responsible for about **30%** of cancer deaths.
- It is characterised by extreme weight loss, fatigue, muscle atrophy, anaemia, anorexia and oedema.
- Mortality is generally the consequence of atrophy of the diaphragm and other respiratory muscles.

## Factors that influence cancer cachexia.

The precise causes are not known.

- **Inflammatory mediators**, particularly **TNF, IL-1 and IL-6**, have important roles. They act directly on skeletal muscle cells, increasing degradation of major structural proteins such as myosin heavy chain through ubiquitination followed by proteolysis in the proteasome.
- **Growth differentiation factor-15 (GDF-15)**, a protein secreted by tumour cells and macrophages, binds to the receptor GFRAL in the brainstem to suppress appetite.
- A multiplicity of factors is involved, because therapies directed against individual cytokines have not been effective in reversing cachexia.

## Define paraneoplastic syndrome.

**Paraneoplastic syndromes** are symptom complexes in individuals with cancer that cannot be explained by tumour spread, or by the release of hormones indigenous to the tissue from which the tumour arose.

## Incidence of paraneoplastic syndrome in cancer patients.

Paraneoplastic syndromes occur in about **10%** of persons with cancer.

They are important to recognise because they:

- may be the earliest manifestation of an occult neoplasm
- can cause significant clinical problems and may even be lethal
- may mimic metastatic disease and therefore confound treatment

## Paraneoplastic syndromes with examples of associated cancers.

| Syndrome | Associated cancer | Mechanism |
|---|---|---|
| **Cushing syndrome** [glucocorticoid excess] | Small cell carcinoma of lung | ACTH or ACTH-like substance |
| Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion | Small cell carcinoma of lung, intracranial neoplasms | Antidiuretic hormone or atrial natriuretic hormones |
| **Hypercalcaemia** | Squamous cell carcinoma of lung, breast carcinoma | Parathyroid hormone–related protein, TGF-α, TNF, IL-1 |
| **Hypoglycaemia** | Ovarian carcinoma | Insulin or insulin-like substance |
| **Polycythaemia** | Renal carcinoma | Erythropoietin |
| Myasthenia and disorders of the central and peripheral nervous system | Bronchogenic carcinoma, breast carcinoma | Immunologic |
| **Acanthosis nigricans** | Gastric carcinoma | Immunologic; secretion of epidermal growth factor |
| **Dermatomyositis** | Bronchogenic carcinoma | Immunologic |
| Hypertrophic osteoarthropathy and clubbing of the fingers | Bronchogenic carcinoma | Unknown |
| **Venous thrombosis (Trousseau phenomenon [migratory thrombophlebitis associated with cancer])** | Pancreatic carcinoma, bronchogenic carcinoma | Tumour products that activate clotting |
| **Disseminated intravascular coagulation** | Acute promyelocytic leukaemia, prostatic carcinoma | Tumour products that activate clotting |
| **Red cell aplasia** | Thymic neoplasms | Immunologic |
| **Nonbacterial thrombotic endocarditis** | Advanced mucin-secreting adenocarcinomas | Mechanism not established |

## Grading of cancer.

**Grading** of a cancer is based on the degree of differentiation of the tumour cells and, in some cancers, the number of mitoses or architectural features.

- Grading schemes have evolved for each type of malignancy and generally range from **two** categories (low grade and high grade) to **four**.
- The criteria differ between tumours, but all attempt to judge the extent to which the tumour cells resemble or fail to resemble their normal counterparts.
- It rests on the idea that behaviour and differentiation are related: poorly differentiated tumours behave more aggressively.
- The correlation between histologic appearance and biologic behaviour is less than perfect. To avoid spurious quantification, a neoplasm is often described in words, for example **well-differentiated, mucin-secreting adenocarcinoma of the stomach** or **poorly differentiated pancreatic adenocarcinoma**.

## Staging of cancer.
