2. **Electrocardiogram.** A transmural infarct shows ST elevation (STEMI) and a subendocardial infarct does not (NSTEMI). Small infarcts may give non-specific changes or none.
3. **Clinical presentation.** Chest pain lasting over 30 minutes, crushing or squeezing, with sweating, nausea, vomiting and dyspnoea. Up to 25% of cases are painless.

## What are the cardiac markers/enzymes of MI (with change over time)?

The clinically useful markers are the **cardiac-specific troponins T and I**.

| Marker | Rise | Peak | Duration of elevation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cardiac troponins T and I** | 2 to 4 hours | 24 to 48 hours | 7 to 10 days |

With reperfusion, troponin levels may be higher and peak earlier because of faster washout from the necrotic tissue.

## Why is troponin the best indicator for MI?

1. **Cardiac specific.** Troponins T and I are proteins that regulate calcium-mediated contraction of cardiac muscle, so a rise reflects myocardial damage.
2. **Sensitive.** They detect irreversible myocyte damage sensitively, because they leak out of necrotic cells once the sarcolemma is disrupted.
3. **Diagnostic threshold.** Myocardial injury is established when levels exceed threshold levels.
4. **Long window.** They begin to rise at 2 to 4 hours, peak at 24 to 48 hours and remain elevated 7 to 10 days, so late presentation can still be diagnosed.

**Limitation.** Acute rises also occur in myocarditis and myocardial trauma, and low-level elevations ("troponin leak") in heart failure, pulmonary embolus, renal failure and sepsis. Serial measurements help, because these do not follow the time course of MI.

## 50-year-old man with severe central chest pain and sweating followed by transient unconsciousness — Likely diagnosis? Laboratory investigations to confirm that diagnosis?

**Likely diagnosis: acute myocardial infarction.** Prolonged crushing central chest pain with sweating is the classic presentation, and syncope may come from an arrhythmia, which complicates about 90% of MIs.

**Investigations to confirm**

1. **Cardiac troponin T or I**, raised above threshold. Rises in 2 to 4 hours, peaks at 24 to 48 hours, stays up 7 to 10 days. Serial measurement helps distinguish other causes of troponin rise.
2. **Electrocardiogram**, with ST elevation (STEMI) in a transmural infarct or no ST elevation (NSTEMI) in a subendocardial one.
3. The clinical picture itself.

## A patient has severe central chest pain, sweating, ± syncope. What is the probable diagnosis?

**Acute myocardial infarction**, caused most often by rupture or erosion of a coronary atheromatous plaque with superimposed thrombosis. The pain is prolonged (more than 30 minutes), crushing or squeezing, with sweating, nausea and dyspnoea. Syncope can be due to an arrhythmia or pump failure.

## Same: what investigations/expected biochemical findings confirm it?

1. **Cardiac troponin T or I** raised above the diagnostic threshold, beginning 2 to 4 hours after onset, peaking at 24 to 48 hours and remaining elevated for 7 to 10 days.
2. **ECG**: ST elevation (STEMI) or none (NSTEMI).
3. **Serial troponin** measurements to separate MI from other causes of mild rise.

## A 70y man has long-standing hypertension. What type of adaptive change may occur in his heart?

**Left ventricular hypertrophy** (concentric), a pathologic adaptation to the pressure overload of chronic hypertension. It leads to **systemic (left-sided) hypertensive heart disease**.

- Myocytes increase in size without new cells.
- The heart is heavy (may exceed 500 g) with a left ventricular wall over 2.0 cm thick, initially without dilation.
- Increased interstitial connective tissue makes the wall stiff, impairing diastolic filling and enlarging the left atrium.
- It is maladaptive in the end: myocardial demand ischaemia, dilation, heart failure and sometimes sudden death.
- Control of hypertension can prevent hypertrophy and even cause regression.

## Same: mention physiological conditions where this change can occur.

Hypertrophy is physiologic when it is driven by increased functional demand or by hormones and growth factors.

1. **Uterus in pregnancy.** Smooth muscle hypertrophy stimulated by oestrogen.
2. **Skeletal muscle of bodybuilders**, from increased demand.

## What are the criteria for diagnosis of rheumatic fever?

Diagnosis follows the **revised Jones criteria** [major and minor criteria for rheumatic fever]: **evidence of a preceding group A streptococcal infection**, plus **two major manifestations, or one major and two minor manifestations**.

**Major manifestations**

1. Migratory polyarthritis of the large joints.
2. Pancarditis (myocarditis, pericarditis or endocarditis).
3. Subcutaneous nodules, typically on extensor surfaces of the limbs.
4. Erythema marginatum, an irregular circinate skin rash.
5. Sydenham chorea, a neurological disorder with rapid involuntary movements.

**Minor manifestations** are non-specific: fever, arthralgia and raised acute-phase reactants.

**Evidence of preceding infection.** Pharyngeal cultures are usually negative by the time of illness, but antibodies to streptococcal enzymes such as streptolysin O and DNase B are found in most patients.

The criteria are evolving and are applied differently in low-risk and high-risk settings.

## Give the pathogenesis of rheumatic fever.

Acute rheumatic fever is an **immune response to group A streptococcal antigens that cross-react with host proteins**.

1. It follows group A streptococcal pharyngitis (sometimes skin infection) after a delay of 2 to 3 weeks, the time needed to generate an immune response.
2. Antibodies and CD4⁺ T cells against streptococcal **M proteins** recognise cardiac antigens in the pericardium, myocardium and valves.
3. Antibody binding activates complement and recruits neutrophils and macrophages; cytokines from stimulated T cells activate macrophages, for example within **Aschoff bodies** [granulomas of rheumatic carditis].
4. Damage is thus from antibody-mediated and T cell-mediated reactions.
5. Streptococci are completely absent from the lesions, as expected for an immunological disease.
6. Only 0.3% to 3% of infected people develop it, so intrinsic genetic susceptibility is likely.
7. Chronic fibrotic lesions are the result of healing and scarring after the acute inflammation.

## Which valves are commonly involved in rheumatic fever?

- The **mitral valve** is virtually always involved in chronic rheumatic heart disease. It is affected alone in about two-thirds of cases.
- The **mitral and aortic valves together** are affected in another 25%.
- The **tricuspid valve** is infrequently involved and the **pulmonary valve** only rarely.

In acute rheumatic fever the left-sided valves show fibrinoid necrosis with small (1 to 2 mm) vegetations called verrucae along the lines of closure.

## What are the complications of rheumatic fever?

1. **Acute carditis.** Pericarditis with friction rub, myocarditis with arrhythmias and cardiac dilation, which can cause functional mitral regurgitation or heart failure. About 1% to 2% die of fulminant cardiac involvement.
2. **Recurrent attacks.** With each streptococcal infection the same manifestations return, and valve damage is cumulative.
3. **Chronic rheumatic heart disease.** Mitral leaflet thickening, commissural fusion and shortening, and thickening and fusion of the tendinous cords, giving "fish mouth" **mitral stenosis**. MacCallum plaques may form in the left atrium.
4. **Late effects.** Heart murmurs, cardiac hypertrophy and dilation, heart failure, atrial fibrillation (particularly with mitral stenosis), left atrial mural thrombi with thromboembolism, and **infective endocarditis**.
5. **Lung changes** from long-standing congestion in mitral stenosis.

## Write a short note on Aschoff nodule (myocardial changes, giant cell).

**Aschoff bodies** [granulomas of rheumatic carditis] are the distinctive lesions of **acute rheumatic fever** in the heart.

- Each is a focal inflammatory lesion of **T lymphocytes**, occasional **plasma cells** and plump activated macrophages called **Anitschkow cells** [caterpillar-nucleus histiocytes of rheumatic carditis].
- Anitschkow cells have abundant cytoplasm and a central round-to-ovoid nucleus (occasionally binucleate) in which the chromatin condenses into a central slender wavy ribbon, so they are called "caterpillar cells".
- They may be found with diffuse inflammation in any of the three layers of the heart, causing pericarditis, myocarditis and endocarditis (**pancarditis**).
- They reflect cytokine-driven macrophage activation in an immune-mediated reaction.

## A child has fever, migratory large-joint pain after sore throat. What is your provisional diagnosis?

**Acute rheumatic fever**, which follows group A streptococcal pharyngitis after a latent period of 10 days to 6 weeks and occurs most often in children aged 5 to 15 years. Migratory polyarthritis of the large joints, in which one large joint after another becomes painful and swollen for days and then settles without residual disability, is a major manifestation, and fever is a minor one.

## Same: how will you diagnose the case?

By the **revised Jones criteria** [major and minor criteria for rheumatic fever]: evidence of a preceding group A streptococcal infection, plus two major or one major and two minor manifestations.

1. **Evidence of streptococcal infection.** Raised antibodies to streptolysin O and DNase B (pharyngeal culture is usually negative by then).
2. **Major criteria**: migratory polyarthritis, pancarditis, subcutaneous nodules, erythema marginatum, Sydenham chorea.
3. **Minor criteria**: fever, arthralgia, raised acute-phase reactants.
4. **Look for carditis**: pericardial friction rub, tachycardia, arrhythmia, murmurs.

## What is vegetation?

A **vegetation** is a lesion on a heart valve formed of fibrin and thrombotic debris. In **infective endocarditis** it also contains inflammatory cells and microorganisms and is friable, bulky and destructive.

## What is subacute infective endocarditis?

**Subacute infective endocarditis** is an insidious infection of previously **deformed heart valves** by organisms of **lower virulence**, such as viridans streptococci, with less valve destruction than acute endocarditis.

## Write the types/classification of infective endocarditis with infective agents.

Traditionally IE is classed on clinical grounds as **acute** or **subacute**, based on severity, tempo (microbial virulence) and whether there is underlying valve disease. The two form a spectrum.

| Type | Valve | Organism | Course |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Acute** | Previously normal | Highly virulent, for example Staphylococcus aureus | Rapid, destructive; antibiotics alone often fail and surgery may be needed |
| **Subacute** | Deformed or damaged | Low virulence, for example viridans streptococci | Protracted over weeks to months; often cured by antibiotics alone |

**Other agents**

- Viridans streptococci cause 50% to 60% of cases on previously damaged native valves.
- S. aureus causes most cases overall, including those in intravenous drug users.
- Enterococci and the HACEK group (Haemophilus, Actinobacillus, Cardiobacterium, Eikenella, Kingella).
- Rarely gram-negative bacilli and fungi.
- **Prosthetic valve endocarditis**: within 1 to 2 months of surgery, skin flora (S. aureus, S. epidermidis); a year or more later, streptococci and S. aureus.
- About 10% are culture negative.

## Give the pathogenesis of infective endocarditis.

1. **Predisposing cardiac lesions.** Rheumatic heart disease with valve scarring was historically the main one. It is now replaced by mitral valve prolapse, degenerative calcific stenosis, bicuspid aortic valve, prosthetic valves and congenital defects.
2. **Bacteraemia or fungaemia**, the foremost predisposing factor. The source may be an infection elsewhere, a dental or surgical procedure, a contaminated needle in intravenous drug users, or trivial breaks in the epithelial barriers of gut, mouth or skin.
3. **Organisms and valve.** Low-virulence viridans streptococci infect damaged valves; virulent S. aureus infects healthy or damaged valves.
4. **Vegetation formation.** The organisms lodge on the valve and form vegetations of fibrin, thrombotic debris, inflammatory cells and organisms, with destruction of the underlying tissue. Organisms deep in the vegetation are hard to release into the blood or reach with antibiotics.
5. **Prevention.** Antibiotic prophylaxis lowers risk in patients with valve abnormalities or known bacteraemia.

## Mention the complications of infective endocarditis.

Complications generally begin within the first few weeks.

1. **Valve destruction** and **ring abscess**, where a vegetation erodes into the myocardium (invasion of the myocardium and conduction system carries a poor outlook).
2. **Embolisation.** Fragments with virulent organisms cause abscesses where they lodge, giving septic infarcts and **mycotic aneurysms**; systemic embolisation bodes ill.
3. **Glomerulonephritis** from deposition of antigen-antibody complexes.
4. **Sepsis** and **arrhythmias.**
5. **Vascular and immunological signs**: splinter haemorrhages, Janeway lesions [small painless haemorrhagic macules on palms and soles], Osler nodes [tender nodules in the digit pulp], Roth spots [retinal haemorrhages with pale centres]. These are now less common because of earlier treatment.
6. **Outcome.** Untreated IE is generally fatal. With 6 weeks or more of antibiotics, or valve replacement, mortality falls. Cure is 98% with low-virulence organisms such as S. viridans, 60% to 90% with enterococci and S. aureus, and half of patients with gram-negative bacilli or fungi die.

## Define neutrophilia.

**Neutrophilia** (neutrophilic leucocytosis) is an increase in the number of neutrophils in the blood. The adult reference range for neutrophils is 1,400 to 6,500 per microlitre.

## Name four important causes of neutrophilic leukocytosis.

**Causes of neutrophilic leucocytosis**

- **Acute bacterial infections**, especially those caused by pyogenic organisms.
- **Sterile inflammation** caused by tissue necrosis, for example myocardial infarction and burns.
- **Chronic infection or inflammation.**
- **Paraneoplastic**, for example Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells].
- **Myeloproliferative neoplasms**, for example chronic myeloid leukaemia.

**Mechanisms**

- Increased release from marrow stores: in acute inflammation, such as infection, there is a rapid increase in the egress of mature granulocytes from the bone marrow pool, mediated through tumour necrosis factor (TNF) and interleukin-1 (IL-1).
- Increased marrow production: in prolonged infection or inflammation, IL-1, TNF and other mediators stimulate macrophages, marrow stromal cells and T cells to produce more haematopoietic growth factors; these increase proliferation and differentiation of committed granulocytic progenitors and sustain neutrophil production over several days. Granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF) induces neutrophilia.
- Chronic infection or inflammation and paraneoplastic causes are growth factor dependent; myeloproliferative neoplasms are growth factor independent.

## Write a short note on eosinophilia.

**Definition:** eosinophilic leucocytosis, an increase in the number of eosinophils in the blood; the adult reference range is 0 to 500 per microlitre.

**Aetiology**

- **Allergic disorders**, such as asthma and hay fever.
- **Parasitic infestations.**
- **Drug reactions.**
- **Certain malignancies**, for example Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] and some non-Hodgkin lymphomas.
- **Autoimmune disorders**, for example pemphigus and dermatitis herpetiformis.

**Pathogenesis:** growth factors preferentially stimulate single leucocyte types; **interleukin-5** mainly stimulates eosinophil production.

## What are the types of leukaemoid reaction with example?

| Type | Features | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Neutrophilic** | Many immature granulocytes appear in the blood, mimicking myeloid leukaemia | Severe infections |
| **Lymphoid** | Large numbers of activated lymphocytes resembling neoplastic lymphoid cells | Acute viral infections, particularly in children; infectious mononucleosis |

## What do you mean by lymphadenopathy?

Lymphadenopathy is enlargement of lymph nodes. It occurs in 2 patterns:

- **Localised:** nodes in a contiguous grouping, in one body area.
- **Generalised:** nodes in 2 or more non-contiguous regions.

## Common causes of lymphadenopathy.

- **Infections:** bacterial and viral infections of the drained area; tuberculosis and Mycobacterium avium intracellulare; fungal (coccidioidomycosis, histoplasmosis, Candida); parasitic (toxoplasmosis, Chagas disease [Trypanosoma cruzi infection]); cat-scratch disease.
- **Reactive hyperplasia:** acute and chronic nonspecific lymphadenitis; follicular hyperplasia in rheumatoid arthritis, toxoplasmosis and early HIV infection; paracortical hyperplasia in infectious mononucleosis.
- **Autoimmune disease.**
- **Malignancy:** lymphoma, leukaemia, metastatic cancer and head and neck cancers.

## Common causes of lymphadenitis.

**Acute nonspecific lymphadenitis**

- Cervical nodes: infections of the teeth or tonsils.
- Axillary or inguinal nodes: infections of the extremities.
- Mesenteric nodes: acute appendicitis and other inflammatory conditions of the gut, including self-limiting viral infections.
- Generalised: systemic viral infections, particularly in children, and bacteraemia.

**Chronic nonspecific lymphadenitis**

- Many chronic immunological stimuli; common in inguinal and axillary nodes, which are stimulated by immune reactions to trivial injuries and infections of the extremities.
- Follicular hyperplasia: rheumatoid arthritis, toxoplasmosis and early HIV infection.
- Paracortical hyperplasia: acute viral infections, for example infectious mononucleosis.
- Sinus histiocytosis: nodes draining cancers, for example carcinoma of the breast.

## Morphological features of chronic non-specific lymphadenitis / reactive hyperplasia.

Several patterns are often seen within the same node. The nodes are nontender because enlargement is slow and there is no acute inflammation.

1. **Follicular hyperplasia:** large oblong germinal centres (secondary follicles) surrounded by a collar of small resting naive B cells, the **mantle zone**.
   - Germinal centres have a **dark zone** of proliferating blast-like B cells (centroblasts) and a **light zone** of B cells with irregular or cleaved nuclei (centrocytes).
   - Follicular dendritic cells and **tingible body macrophages** containing nuclear debris of apoptotic B cells lie among them.
   - Features favouring reactive over neoplastic follicles: preserved node architecture, including interfollicular T-cell zones and sinusoids; marked variation in follicle shape and size; frequent mitotic figures, phagocytic macrophages, and recognisable light and dark zones.
2. **Paracortical hyperplasia:** expanded T-cell zones with immunoblasts (three to four times the size of resting lymphocytes, with round nuclei, open chromatin and prominent nucleoli); hypertrophy of sinusoidal and vascular endothelial cells, sometimes with macrophages and eosinophils. Exuberant reactions may efface the B-cell follicles.
3. **Sinus histiocytosis (reticular hyperplasia):** increased number and size of endothelial cells lining the sinuses, and increased intrasinusoidal macrophages that expand and distort the sinuses.

## Write down the causes of generalized lymphadenopathy.

- **Systemic viral infections**, particularly in children, and bacteraemia.
- Chronic lymphocytic leukaemia/small lymphocytic lymphoma, in 50% to 60% of symptomatic patients.
- **Follicular lymphoma**, mantle cell lymphoma and peripheral T-cell lymphoma, not otherwise specified.
- **Acute lymphoblastic leukaemia**, from neoplastic infiltration.
- **Tuberculosis** and other mycobacterial, fungal and parasitic infections (for example toxoplasmosis).
- **Autoimmune disease.**
- Generalised lymphadenopathy means nodes in 2 or more non-contiguous regions.

## What are the causes of cervical lymphadenopathy?

- **Acute nonspecific lymphadenitis** from infections of the teeth or tonsils.
- Posterior cervical nodes: localised bacterial and viral infections, and lymphoma.
- **Hodgkin lymphoma** [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] and other lymphomas.
- **Metastatic cancer**, including head and neck cancers.

## What is the etiology of leukaemia?

- **Genetic alterations in cancer genes:** nonrandom chromosomal abnormalities, most commonly translocations, are present in most white cell neoplasms, with driver mutations that block differentiation, drive growth or prevent apoptosis.
- **Inherited factors:** diseases with genomic instability (Bloom syndrome [inherited DNA-helicase defect with sun-sensitive rash and cancer predisposition], Fanconi anaemia [inherited DNA-repair defect causing marrow failure], ataxia telangiectasia) raise the risk of acute leukaemia; **Down syndrome** [trisomy 21] (trisomy 21) and type I neurofibromatosis are associated with childhood leukaemia.
- **Viruses:** HTLV-1 with adult T-cell leukaemia/lymphoma; EBV with a subset of Burkitt lymphoma [high-grade B-cell lymphoma with MYC translocation] and other lymphomas; HHV8 with an unusual B-cell lymphoma presenting as an effusion.
- **Chronic inflammation:** for example Helicobacter pylori with gastric B-cell lymphomas, and gluten-sensitive enteropathy with intestinal T-cell lymphomas.
- **Treatment:** chemotherapy with alkylating agents has been followed by a high incidence of acute myeloid leukaemia.

## Classify AML (FAB classification).

| Type | Name |
|---|---|
| **M0** | Acute myeloblastic leukaemia with minimal differentiation |
| **M1** | Myeloblastic leukaemia without maturation |
| **M2** | Myeloblastic leukaemia with maturation |
| **M3** | Acute promyelocytic leukaemia |
| **M4** | Acute myelomonocytic leukaemia; the myeloblastic and monoblastic components each make up more than 20% of the blasts in the marrow |
| **M5** | Acute monoblastic leukaemia: M5a without differentiation, M5b with differentiation |
| **M6** | Erythroleukaemia |
| **M7** | Acute megakaryoblastic leukaemia |

## What are the common presentations of acute leukaemia/AML?

- **Abrupt, stormy onset** within days to a few weeks of the first symptoms.
- **Marrow failure**: fatigue from anaemia; fever from infections secondary to neutropenia; bleeding from thrombocytopenia.
- **Bleeding**: cutaneous petechiae and ecchymoses, serosal haemorrhages, and mucosal haemorrhages into the gingivae and urinary tract. Procoagulants and fibrinolytic factors released by leukaemic cells, especially in AML with t(15;17), make bleeding worse.
- **Infections**, frequent in the oral cavity, skin, lungs, kidneys, urinary bladder and colon, often caused by opportunists such as fungi, Pseudomonas and commensal organisms.
- **Mass effects from neoplastic infiltration**, more common in ALL: bone pain, generalised lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, hepatomegaly, testicular enlargement, and in T-ALL compression of large vessels and airways in the mediastinum.
- **Central nervous system** manifestations, more common in ALL: headache, vomiting and nerve palsies from meningeal spread.
- In AML, tumours with monocytic differentiation often infiltrate the skin (leukaemia cutis) and gingiva; AML occasionally presents as a localised soft tissue mass, a myeloid sarcoma.

## A child has severe pallor, sore throat, bleeding, lymphadenopathy, blasts in PBF. What is the probable diagnosis?

**Probable diagnosis: acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (ALL)**, the most common cancer of children.

- Pallor from anaemia; sore throat and fever from infections secondary to neutropenia; bleeding from thrombocytopenia; lymphadenopathy from neoplastic infiltration, which is more common in ALL.
- Confirmed by bone marrow examination, generally 20% or more blasts, myeloperoxidase negative, TdT positive in more than 95%, and immunophenotyping.

## Give the blood picture and bone marrow findings of AML.

1. **Peripheral blood:** blast count highly variable; may be more than 100,000 per microlitre but under 10,000 in about 50%; occasionally absent (aleukaemic leukaemia). Anaemia, neutropenia and thrombocytopenia are present.
2. **Bone marrow examination** is essential to exclude acute leukaemia in pancytopenic patients. Generally 20% or more myeloid blasts; the WHO permits the diagnosis with fewer than 20% if a defining genetic aberration is present.
3. **Blast morphology:** myeloblasts have delicate nuclear chromatin, two to four nucleoli and more cytoplasm than lymphoblasts, with fine peroxidase positive azurophilic granules. **Auer rods** [needle-shaped azurophilic inclusions in myeloblasts] (needle-like azurophilic granules) are present in many cases, particularly numerous in t(15;17). Monoblasts have folded or lobulated nuclei, lack Auer rods and are nonspecific esterase positive.
4. **Immunophenotyping:** stains for myeloid-specific antigens confirm the diagnosis, since myeloblasts and lymphoblasts are hard to tell apart by morphology; blasts express CD34, CD33 and in part CD15, but not CD64. Megakaryocytic blasts are detected with antibodies such as GPIIb/IIIa.
5. **Cytogenetics:** abnormalities are detected in 50% to 70% of cases with standard techniques and about 90% with high-resolution banding. Balanced translocations t(8;21), inv(16) and t(15;17) are typical of de novo AML in younger adults; deletions or monosomies of chromosomes 5 and 7 and p53 mutations follow DNA-damaging agents.
6. **DNA sequencing** detects mutations such as NPM1.

## Write down the clinical presentation of ALL.

- **Abrupt, stormy onset** within days to a few weeks of the first symptoms.
- **Marrow failure**: fatigue from anaemia; fever from infections secondary to neutropenia; bleeding from thrombocytopenia.
- **Bleeding**: cutaneous petechiae and ecchymoses, serosal haemorrhages, and mucosal haemorrhages into the gingivae and urinary tract. Procoagulants and fibrinolytic factors released by leukaemic cells, especially in AML with t(15;17), make bleeding worse.
- **Infections**, frequent in the oral cavity, skin, lungs, kidneys, urinary bladder and colon, often caused by opportunists such as fungi, Pseudomonas and commensal organisms.
- **Mass effects from neoplastic infiltration**, more common in ALL: bone pain, generalised lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, hepatomegaly, testicular enlargement, and in T-ALL compression of large vessels and airways in the mediastinum.
- **Central nervous system** manifestations, more common in ALL: headache, vomiting and nerve palsies from meningeal spread.
- In AML, tumours with monocytic differentiation often infiltrate the skin (leukaemia cutis) and gingiva; AML occasionally presents as a localised soft tissue mass, a myeloid sarcoma.

## Give the laboratory diagnosis/bone marrow findings of ALL.

1. **Peripheral blood:** blast count is highly variable; in AML blasts may be more than 100,000 per microlitre but are under 10,000 in about 50% of patients, and occasionally blasts are absent from the blood (aleukaemic leukaemia).
2. **Bone marrow examination** is essential. The marrow is hypercellular and packed with blasts, generally 20% or more of marrow cells.
3. **Morphology of blasts:** lymphoblasts have condensed chromatin, small nucleoli and scant agranular cytoplasm; myeloblasts have delicate chromatin, two to four nucleoli and more cytoplasm with fine azurophilic granules and often Auer rods [needle-shaped azurophilic inclusions in myeloblasts].
4. **Cytochemistry:** myeloperoxidase positive in myeloblasts; lymphoblasts are myeloperoxidase negative and often contain periodic acid–Schiff positive cytoplasmic material; monoblasts are nonspecific esterase positive.
5. **Immunophenotyping by flow cytometry**, which confirms the lineage. Terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase (TdT) is positive in more than 95% of ALL. B-ALL: CD19, CD10, PAX5; T-ALL: CD1, CD2, CD5, CD7. Myeloid blasts: CD34, CD33, CD15 and CD64.
6. **Cytogenetics and molecular tests:** chromosomal aberrations in 50% to 70% of AML by standard techniques; approximately 90% of ALL have numerical or structural chromosomal changes.

## Give the blood picture of ALL.

1. **Peripheral blood:** blast count is highly variable; in AML blasts may be more than 100,000 per microlitre but are under 10,000 in about 50% of patients, and occasionally blasts are absent from the blood (aleukaemic leukaemia).
2. **Bone marrow examination** is essential. The marrow is hypercellular and packed with blasts, generally 20% or more of marrow cells.
3. **Morphology of blasts:** lymphoblasts have condensed chromatin, small nucleoli and scant agranular cytoplasm; myeloblasts have delicate chromatin, two to four nucleoli and more cytoplasm with fine azurophilic granules and often Auer rods [needle-shaped azurophilic inclusions in myeloblasts].
4. **Cytochemistry:** myeloperoxidase positive in myeloblasts; lymphoblasts are myeloperoxidase negative and often contain periodic acid–Schiff positive cytoplasmic material; monoblasts are nonspecific esterase positive.
5. **Immunophenotyping by flow cytometry**, which confirms the lineage. Terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase (TdT) is positive in more than 95% of ALL. B-ALL: CD19, CD10, PAX5; T-ALL: CD1, CD2, CD5, CD7. Myeloid blasts: CD34, CD33, CD15 and CD64.
6. **Cytogenetics and molecular tests:** chromosomal aberrations in 50% to 70% of AML by standard techniques; approximately 90% of ALL have numerical or structural chromosomal changes.

## Write a short note on blast crisis.

- Blast crisis is the terminal event in CML, a picture resembling **acute leukaemia**.
- About 50% of patients first enter an **accelerated phase** after a variable period averaging 3 years, with increasing anaemia and thrombocytopenia, sometimes a rise in basophils, and new cytogenetic abnormalities such as trisomy 8, isochromosome 17q or duplication of the Ph chromosome. Within 6 to 12 months this ends in blast crisis.
- In the other 50%, blast crisis occurs abruptly without an accelerated phase.
- In 70% of crises the blasts are myeloid (**myeloid blast crisis**); in most of the remainder they are of pre-B cell origin (**lymphoid blast crisis**). This is evidence that CML originates from a pluripotent stem cell with both myeloid and lymphoid potential.
- In lymphoid blast crisis, 85% of cases have mutations that interfere with Ikaros, a transcription factor encoded by IKZF1.

## Define chronic leukaemia.

**Leukaemias** are neoplasms that present with widespread involvement of the bone marrow and, usually but not always, the peripheral blood. The chronic leukaemias are **chronic lymphocytic leukaemia**, a B-cell neoplasm, and **chronic myeloid leukaemia**, a myeloproliferative neoplasm distinguished by the BCR::ABL gene.

## Give the clinical manifestations of chronic granulocytic leukaemia.

- **Insidious onset**, mainly in adults, with peak incidence in the fifth and sixth decades.
- Mild to moderate anaemia and hypermetabolism from increased cell turnover cause fatigability, weakness, weight loss and anorexia.
- Dragging sensation in the abdomen from **splenomegaly**, or sudden left upper quadrant pain from splenic infarction.
- Mild hepatomegaly and lymphadenopathy from extramedullary haematopoiesis.
- Slow progression; untreated median survival about 3 years.
- **Accelerated phase** in about 50%, after an average of 3 years: increasing anaemia and thrombocytopenia, sometimes more basophils, and new cytogenetic abnormalities.
- **Blast crisis** within 6 to 12 months, resembling acute leukaemia; in the other 50% it occurs abruptly.

## Give the laboratory diagnosis (blood/bone marrow) of chronic myeloid leukaemia.

1. **Peripheral blood**
   - Leucocytosis, often exceeding 100,000 per microlitre, of neutrophils, band forms, metamyelocytes, myelocytes, eosinophils and basophils.
   - Blasts usually less than 10% of circulating cells.
   - Platelets usually increased, sometimes markedly.
   - Mild to moderate anaemia.
2. **Bone marrow**
   - Markedly hypercellular, with massively increased maturing granulocytic precursors, usually with a raised proportion of eosinophils and basophils.
   - Megakaryocytes increased, usually small and dysplastic.
   - Erythroid progenitors normal or mildly decreased.
   - Scattered **sea-blue histiocytes** (macrophages with abundant wrinkled green-blue cytoplasm).
   - Increased reticulin; overt fibrosis is rare.
3. **Molecular and cytogenetic tests:** the BCR::ABL fusion gene, with the Philadelphia chromosome [t(9;22) producing the BCR-ABL1 fusion] t(9;22), detected by chromosomal analysis, FISH or PCR-based tests; this differentiates CML from other myeloproliferative neoplasms.

## Classify lymphoma.

| Group | Entities |
|---|---|
| **Precursor B-cell neoplasms** | B-cell acute lymphoblastic leukaemia/lymphoma |
| **Peripheral B-cell neoplasms** | Chronic lymphocytic leukaemia/small lymphocytic lymphoma; splenic B-cell lymphomas and leukaemias; marginal zone lymphoma; mantle cell lymphoma; follicular lymphoma; diffuse large B-cell lymphoma; Burkitt lymphoma [high-grade B-cell lymphoma with MYC translocation]; lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma; plasma cell neoplasms |
| **Precursor T-cell neoplasms** | T-cell acute lymphoblastic leukaemia/lymphoma |
| **Peripheral T-cell and NK-cell neoplasms** | T-cell prolymphocytic leukaemia; T-large granular lymphocytic leukaemia; mycosis fungoides; Sézary syndrome [leukaemic cutaneous T-cell lymphoma]; peripheral T-cell lymphoma, not otherwise specified; anaplastic large-cell lymphoma; nodal T-follicular helper cell lymphoma; enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma; subcutaneous panniculitis-like T-cell lymphoma; hepatosplenic T-cell lymphoma; adult T-cell leukaemia/lymphoma; extranodal NK/T-cell lymphoma; NK-cell leukaemia |
| **Hodgkin lymphoma** [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] | Classic: nodular sclerosis, mixed cellularity, lymphocyte-rich, lymphocyte-depleted; nodular lymphocyte predominant |

Lymphomas other than Hodgkin lymphoma are the non-Hodgkin lymphomas.

## Differences between Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphoma.

| Feature | Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] | Non-Hodgkin lymphoma |
|---|---|---|
| Nodal involvement | More often localised to a single axial group of nodes (cervical, mediastinal, para-aortic) | More frequent involvement of multiple peripheral nodes |
| Spread | Orderly, by contiguity | Noncontiguous; disseminates widely and unpredictably early |
| Mesenteric nodes and Waldeyer ring [ring of pharyngeal lymphoid tissue] | Rarely involved | Commonly involved |
| Extranodal presentation | Rare | Common |
| Presentation | Virtually all present as enlarged nontender nodes | Two-thirds present as enlarged nontender nodes |
| Staging | Most useful in guiding therapy | Less useful in guiding therapy |
| Distinctive cell | **Reed-Sternberg cells** [binucleate owl-eye giant cell], with reactive cells making up more than 90% of the tumour | No Reed-Sternberg cells; neoplastic lymphoid cells, 85% to 90% of lymphoid neoplasms being of B-cell origin |

## What do you know about Reed-Sternberg cells?

- **Reed-Sternberg cells** [binucleate owl-eye giant cell] are the neoplastic giant cells that are the distinctive feature of Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells].
- **Origin:** germinal centre or post-germinal centre B cells, shown by clonal IGH gene rearrangements and somatic hypermutation. In classic Hodgkin lymphoma they fail to express most B-cell-specific genes, including immunoglobulin genes.
- **Diagnostic Reed-Sternberg cell:** a large cell (about 45 micrometres) with multiple nuclei or a nucleus with multiple lobes, each with a large inclusion-like nucleolus about the size of a small lymphocyte, and abundant cytoplasm.
- **Variants:** mononuclear, lacunar and lymphohistiocytic (L&H, popcorn) cells.
- **Immunophenotype (classic types):** positive for PAX5, CD15 and CD30; negative for other B-cell markers, T-cell markers and CD45.
- **NF-κB activation** promotes their growth and survival, through EBV latent membrane protein 1 or loss-of-function mutations in IκB or TNF-induced protein 3.
- They secrete cytokines and chemokines (interleukin-5, interleukin-10, M-CSF, eotaxin) that attract reactive cells; PD-L1 and PD-L2 on them blunt cytotoxic T-cell responses.
- In classic forms they die by shrinking and becoming pyknotic, called **mummification**.
- Similar cells may be seen in infectious mononucleosis, solid tissue cancers and certain non-Hodgkin lymphomas.

## Classification of Hodgkin's lymphoma.

**Classic Hodgkin lymphoma** [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells]

1. Nodular sclerosis.
2. Mixed cellularity.
3. Lymphocyte-rich.
4. Lymphocyte-depleted.

**Nonclassic**

5. Nodular lymphocyte predominant.

In the first four the Reed-Sternberg cells [binucleate owl-eye giant cell] have a similar distinctive immunophenotype. In nodular lymphocyte predominant, the Reed-Sternberg cells have a B-cell immunophenotype different from the classic types.

## How will you proceed to diagnose a case of lymphoma?

1. **Suspicion** is clinical: most lymphoid neoplasms present as enlarged nontender lymph nodes, often more than 2 cm.
2. **Histological examination of a lymph node** or other involved tissue is required for diagnosis.
3. **Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells]:** diagnosis rests on identifying Reed-Sternberg cells [binucleate owl-eye giant cell] in a background of nonneoplastic inflammatory cells, confirmed by their immunohistochemical profile.
4. **Markers:** antibodies to lineage-specific proteins characterise the tumour; analysis of genetic alterations complements the morphology.
5. **Antigen receptor gene rearrangement studies** separate monoclonal (malignant) from polyclonal (reactive) proliferations.
6. **Staging:** physical examination, radiological imaging of the abdomen, pelvis and chest, and bone marrow biopsy.

## A 20y male has multiple painless cervical/axillary lymph nodes. What is your provisional diagnosis?

**Provisional diagnosis: lymphoma, most probably Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells].**

- Lymphomas most commonly present as painless lymphadenopathy.
- Hodgkin lymphoma has a peak in young adults and arises in a single node or chain of nodes; cervical and axillary nodes are commonly involved.

## Same: what tests can you do on the lymph nodes?

1. **Excision biopsy with histological examination** of a node: the diagnosis of Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] depends on Reed-Sternberg cells [binucleate owl-eye giant cell] in a background of nonneoplastic inflammatory cells.
2. **Immunohistochemistry:** Reed-Sternberg cells of classic types are positive for PAX5, CD15 and CD30 and negative for CD45.
3. **Antigen receptor gene rearrangement** studies, to separate monoclonal from polyclonal proliferations.
4. **Staging:** imaging of the chest, abdomen and pelvis, and bone marrow biopsy.

## Give the WHO classification of Hodgkin lymphoma.

| Subtype | Frequency | Key features |
|---|---|---|
| **Nodular sclerosis** | 65% to 70% | Lacunar cells; collagen bands dividing the node into nodules |
| **Mixed cellularity** | 20% to 25% | Many diagnostic Reed-Sternberg cells [binucleate owl-eye giant cell]; diffuse effacement by mixed infiltrate |
| **Lymphocyte-rich** | Uncommon | Reactive lymphocytes predominate |
| **Lymphocyte-depleted** | Less than 5% | Few lymphocytes; many Reed-Sternberg cells or pleomorphic variants |
| **Nodular lymphocyte predominant** (nonclassic) | About 5% | L&H (popcorn) cells; B-cell markers |

## Describe in brief the morphological features of Hodgkin's disease.

- **Reed-Sternberg cells** [binucleate owl-eye giant cell] and variants (mononuclear, lacunar, lymphohistiocytic) in a background of nonneoplastic cells: reactive lymphocytes, macrophages and granulocytes, which make up more than 90% of the tumour cellularity.
- Diagnostic Reed-Sternberg cells: large, with multiple nuclei or a multilobed nucleus, each lobe with a large inclusion-like nucleolus, and abundant cytoplasm.
- **Nodular sclerosis:** lacunar cells; bands of collagen dividing the node into nodules.
- **Mixed cellularity:** node diffusely effaced by T cells, eosinophils, plasma cells and macrophages, with plentiful Reed-Sternberg cells.
- **Lymphocyte-rich:** reactive lymphocytes predominate; node diffusely effaced.
- **Lymphocyte-depleted:** few lymphocytes, many Reed-Sternberg cells or pleomorphic variants.
- **Nodular lymphocyte predominant:** nodules of small lymphocytes and macrophages; L&H (popcorn) cells; classic Reed-Sternberg cells usually absent.
- Involvement of spleen, liver, bone marrow and other organs appears as irregular tumour nodules resembling those in the nodes.

## Describe the Reed-Sternberg cell and its variants.

| Cell | Features | Seen in |
|---|---|---|
| **Diagnostic Reed-Sternberg cell** [binucleate owl-eye giant cell] | Large cell, multiple nuclei or one nucleus with several lobes, each with a large inclusion-like nucleolus about the size of a small lymphocyte; abundant cytoplasm | Classic Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] |
| **Mononuclear variant** | Single nucleus with a large inclusion-like nucleolus | Classic Hodgkin lymphoma |
| **Lacunar cell** | Delicate, folded or multilobed nucleus; abundant pale cytoplasm that is disrupted in sectioning, leaving the nucleus in an empty space (lacuna) | Nodular sclerosis |
| **Lymphohistiocytic (L&H) variant, popcorn cell** | Multilobed polypoid nucleus, inconspicuous nucleoli, moderately abundant cytoplasm; B-cell markers CD20 and BCL6, negative for CD15 and CD30 | Nodular lymphocyte predominant |

In classic forms the cells die by shrinking and becoming pyknotic, called **mummification**.

## Write a short note on Hodgkin's lymphoma.

**Definition:** a group of lymphoid neoplasms that differ from non-Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells]. It arises in a single node or chain of nodes and spreads first to contiguous lymphoid tissues; the distinctive feature is the **Reed-Sternberg cell** [binucleate owl-eye giant cell], which induces reactive lymphocytes, macrophages and granulocytes that make up more than 90% of the tumour. Average age at diagnosis is 39, with a bimodal distribution, peaks in young adults and in adults older than 55.

**Pathogenesis**

- Reed-Sternberg cells derive from germinal centre or post-germinal centre B cells.
- **NF-κB activation**, through EBV latent membrane protein 1 or loss-of-function mutations in IκB or TNF-induced protein 3.
- EBV in a subset of classic cases.
- Gains of REL on chromosome 2p and of PD-L1 and PD-L2 genes on chromosome 9p.
- Reed-Sternberg cells secrete cytokines (interleukin-5, interleukin-10, M-CSF) and chemokines that attract reactive cells; PD-L1 and PD-L2 make the host immune response ineffective.

**Morphology:** Reed-Sternberg cells and variants in a reactive background. WHO subtypes: nodular sclerosis (65% to 70%), mixed cellularity (20% to 25%), lymphocyte-rich, lymphocyte-depleted (less than 5%), and nodular lymphocyte predominant (about 5%).

**Clinical features**

- Painless lymphadenopathy.
- Fever, night sweats and weight loss in stage III and IV disease, mixed cellularity and lymphocyte-depleted subtypes.
- Cutaneous anergy from depressed cell-mediated immunity, from interleukin-10 suppressing Th1 responses.
- Stereotyped spread: nodes, then spleen, then liver, then marrow and other tissues.
- Stage is the most important prognostic variable; cure rate close to 90% in stages I and IIA.

**Complications of treatment:** second cancers after radiotherapy (lung cancer, melanoma, breast cancer) and acute myeloid leukaemia after alkylating agent chemotherapy.

## Write a short note on nodular sclerosis variety of Hodgkin's disease.

- Most common form of Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells], 65% to 70% of cases.
- **Lacunar variant Reed-Sternberg cells** [binucleate owl-eye giant cell], with **collagen deposited in bands** that divide the node into circumscribed nodules; fibrosis may be scant or abundant.
- Polymorphous background of T cells, eosinophils, plasma cells and macrophages; diagnostic Reed-Sternberg cells are usually uncommon.
- Reed-Sternberg cells are positive for PAX5, CD15 and CD30, and negative for other B-cell markers, T-cell markers and CD45.
- Uncommonly associated with EBV.
- Equal in males and females; involves lower cervical, supraclavicular and mediastinal nodes of adolescents or young adults.
- Prognosis is excellent.

## How does Hodgkin lymphoma differ from non-Hodgkin lymphoma?

| Feature | Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] | Non-Hodgkin lymphoma |
|---|---|---|
| Nodal involvement | More often localised to a single axial group of nodes (cervical, mediastinal, para-aortic) | More frequent involvement of multiple peripheral nodes |
| Spread | Orderly, by contiguity | Noncontiguous; disseminates widely and unpredictably early |
| Mesenteric nodes and Waldeyer ring [ring of pharyngeal lymphoid tissue] | Rarely involved | Commonly involved |
| Extranodal presentation | Rare | Common |
| Presentation | Virtually all present as enlarged nontender nodes | Two-thirds present as enlarged nontender nodes |
| Staging | Most useful in guiding therapy | Less useful in guiding therapy |
| Distinctive cell | **Reed-Sternberg cells** [binucleate owl-eye giant cell], with reactive cells making up more than 90% of the tumour | No Reed-Sternberg cells; neoplastic lymphoid cells, 85% to 90% of lymphoid neoplasms being of B-cell origin |

## What is paraproteinaemia?

**Paraproteinaemia** (monoclonal gammopathy) is the term used for the abnormal immunoglobulins associated with plasma cell neoplasms; neoplastic plasma cells virtually always secrete monoclonal immunoglobulin or immunoglobulin fragments. A monoclonal immunoglobulin identified in the blood is called an **M component**.

## Give 4 common causes of paraproteinaemia.

1. **Multiple myeloma** (plasma cell myeloma), with its variants solitary plasmacytoma and smouldering myeloma.
2. **Waldenström macroglobulinaemia** [lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma with IgM paraprotein], in which high levels of IgM cause hyperviscosity.
3. **Heavy-chain disease**, with free heavy-chain fragments.
4. **Immunoglobulin-related (AL) amyloidosis**, from monoclonal plasma cells secreting light chains.
5. Monoclonal gammopathy of undetermined significance (MGUS).

## What is myeloma?

**Multiple myeloma** is a plasma cell neoplasm commonly associated with lytic bone lesions, hypercalcaemia, renal failure and acquired immune abnormalities.

## Pathophysiological and biochemical features of myeloma.

**Pathophysiology**

- Rearrangements of the **IGH locus on chromosome 14q32** with proto-oncogenes, including cyclin D1 and cyclin D3; deletion of **17p** (the TP53 locus) gives a poor outcome; rearrangement of MYC in aggressive forms such as plasma cell leukaemia; mutations in the NF-κB pathway.
- **Interleukin-6** is the main growth factor, made by the tumour cells and by marrow stromal cells.
- **Bone destruction:** myeloma-derived MIP1a (CCL3) increases osteoclast formation, and Wnt pathway modulators inhibit osteoblasts. The net result is increased bone resorption, with hypercalcaemia and pathological fractures.
- **Excess immunoglobulin of a single type** with abnormal physicochemical properties: hyperviscosity in about 7% (usually IgA or IgG3 subtype), and AL amyloidosis from certain light chains (lambda 6 and lambda 3 families).
- **Suppression of normal humoral immunity** leads to recurrent bacterial infections; cellular immunity is relatively unaffected.
- **Renal failure** in up to 50%; the most important factor is Bence Jones [free monoclonal light chains in the urine] proteinuria, as light chains are toxic to renal tubular epithelial cells.

**Biochemical features**

- **M protein** in the serum, usually more than 3 g/dL; IgG in about 55% and IgA in about 25%.
- **Bence Jones protein** in the urine, usually more than 6 mg/dL.
- Free light chains alone in about 20%; about 1% are nonsecretory.
- **Hypercalcaemia.**
- Decreased levels of normal polyclonal immunoglobulins.

## Laboratory diagnosis of myeloma.

1. **Serum and urine electrophoresis:** a sharp abnormal protein spike (M protein) in place of the broad polyclonal band; confirmed and typed by **immunofixation**.
2. **Immunoglobulins:** increased in the blood in 99% of patients, and/or light chains (**Bence Jones proteins** [free monoclonal light chains in the urine]) in the urine. Most myelomas have more than 3 g/dL of serum immunoglobulin and/or more than 6 mg/dL of urine Bence Jones protein. Free light chains alone are present in about 20%, and about 1% are nonsecretory.
3. **Bone marrow examination:** clonal plasma cells, usually more than 30% of the cellularity; positive for CD138 and often CD56, with cytoplasmic kappa or lambda light chain.
4. **CRAB criteria:** hypercalcaemia, renal dysfunction, anaemia and bone lesions, assessed by calcium levels, renal function and blood counts.
5. **Radiography:** punched-out lytic defects, usually 1 to 4 cm in diameter; sometimes diffuse osteopenia.
6. **Peripheral blood smear:** **rouleaux formation**; rarely plasma cell leukaemia.

## What is Bence-Jones protein?

Free light chains, made in excess by neoplastic plasma cells, are small and are excreted in the urine, where they are called **Bence Jones proteins** [free monoclonal light chains in the urine].

## Classify immunoproliferative disorders.

| Disorder | Features |
|---|---|
| **Multiple myeloma** (plasma cell myeloma) | Tumour masses scattered through the skeletal system; variants are **solitary plasmacytoma** (single mass in bone or soft tissue) and **smouldering myeloma** (no symptoms, high plasma M component) |
| **Waldenström macroglobulinaemia** [lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma with IgM paraprotein] | High IgM levels cause hyperviscosity; usually with lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma |
| **Heavy-chain disease** | Synthesis and secretion of free heavy-chain fragments; seen with lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma and small bowel marginal zone lymphoma (Mediterranean lymphoma) |
| **Immunoglobulin-related (AL) amyloidosis** | Monoclonal plasma cells secrete light chains, usually lambda, that are deposited as amyloid |
| **Monoclonal gammopathy of undetermined significance (MGUS)** | No symptoms; small to moderately large M component; may transform to multiple myeloma or another symptomatic plasma cell neoplasm |

## Waldenström's macroglobulinaemia (in short).

**Definition:** a syndrome in which high levels of IgM lead to symptoms from hyperviscosity of the blood. It occurs in older adults, most commonly with **lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma**, a B-cell neoplasm of the sixth or seventh decade in which many tumour cells differentiate into plasma cells that secrete monoclonal IgM.

**Pathogenesis:** virtually all cases have acquired mutations in MYD88, which activates NF-κB and may promote tumour cell growth and survival.

**Morphology**

- Marrow infiltrate of lymphocytes, plasma cells and plasmacytoid lymphocytes, often with mast cell hyperplasia.
- Periodic acid–Schiff positive inclusions of immunoglobulin in the cytoplasm (**Russell bodies** [eosinophilic immunoglobulin inclusions in plasma cells]) or nucleus (**Dutcher bodies** [intranuclear immunoglobulin inclusions in plasma cells]) of some plasma cells.
- At diagnosis the tumour has usually spread to lymph nodes, spleen and liver.

**Clinical features**

- Weakness, fatigue and weight loss; about half have lymphadenopathy, hepatomegaly and splenomegaly; anaemia from marrow infiltration.
- About 10% have autoimmune haemolysis from cold agglutinins (IgM antibodies that bind red cells below 37°C).
- **Hyperviscosity syndrome:** visual impairment with venous congestion, retinal haemorrhages and exudates; headache, dizziness, deafness and stupor; bleeding, from IgM complexes with clotting factors and interference with platelet function.
- **Cryoglobulinaemia:** precipitation of IgM in the cold, with Raynaud phenomenon [episodic digital vasospasm] and cold urticaria.
- Renal failure and amyloidosis from free light chains are rare, and bone destruction does not occur.

**Course:** indolent; plasmapheresis relieves the symptoms of high IgM; median survival is greater than 10 years.

## How will you diagnose a case of multiple myeloma?

1. **Serum and urine electrophoresis:** a sharp abnormal protein spike (M protein) in place of the broad polyclonal band; confirmed and typed by **immunofixation**.
2. **Immunoglobulins:** increased in the blood in 99% of patients, and/or light chains (**Bence Jones proteins** [free monoclonal light chains in the urine]) in the urine. Most myelomas have more than 3 g/dL of serum immunoglobulin and/or more than 6 mg/dL of urine Bence Jones protein. Free light chains alone are present in about 20%, and about 1% are nonsecretory.
3. **Bone marrow examination:** clonal plasma cells, usually more than 30% of the cellularity; positive for CD138 and often CD56, with cytoplasmic kappa or lambda light chain.
4. **CRAB criteria:** hypercalcaemia, renal dysfunction, anaemia and bone lesions, assessed by calcium levels, renal function and blood counts.
5. **Radiography:** punched-out lytic defects, usually 1 to 4 cm in diameter; sometimes diffuse osteopenia.
6. **Peripheral blood smear:** **rouleaux formation**; rarely plasma cell leukaemia.

## Give the bone marrow findings of multiple myeloma.

- **Plasma cells increased**, usually more than 30% of the cellularity; they may infiltrate subtly or completely replace the normal marrow elements.
- Malignant plasma cells have a perinuclear clearing from a prominent Golgi apparatus and an eccentrically placed nucleus.
- Cytological forms range from relatively normal-looking plasma cells, plasmablasts with vesicular chromatin and a prominent single nucleolus, to bizarre multinucleated cells.
- Cytoplasmic immunoglobulin accumulates in variants: **flame cells** with fiery red cytoplasm, **Mott cells** [plasma cells filled with Russell bodies] with grape-like cytoplasmic droplets, and cells with fibrils, crystalline rods and globules.
- Globular inclusions are **Russell bodies** [eosinophilic immunoglobulin inclusions in plasma cells] if cytoplasmic and **Dutcher bodies** [intranuclear immunoglobulin inclusions in plasma cells] if nuclear.
- Immunophenotype: positive for CD138 and often CD56, with cytoplasmic kappa or lambda light chain.

## Why is ESR sky-high in multiple myeloma?

- High levels of M protein in the blood cause red cells to stick to one another in linear arrays on smears, called **rouleaux formation**.
- Rouleaux formation is characteristic but not specific; it is also seen in other conditions with high immunoglobulin levels, such as lupus erythematosus and early HIV infection.

## An elderly patient has low back/bone pain, punched-out lesions, sky-high ESR. What is your probable diagnosis?

**Probable diagnosis: multiple myeloma.**

- Bone pain and pathological fractures, from plasma cell tumours that erode bone; the radiographic defects are punched-out and 1 to 4 cm across.
- The high ESR fits the M protein in the blood, which makes red cells stick together in rouleaux.
- Confirmed by bone marrow examination and by serum and urine M protein studies.

## Write a short note on Bence-Jones protein and its detection in urine.

**Definition:** free light chains made in excess by neoplastic plasma cells are small enough to be excreted in the urine, where they are called **Bence Jones proteins** [free monoclonal light chains in the urine].

**Detection**

- Light chains in the urine are found as an abnormal protein spike on **urine electrophoresis**, then characterised by **immunofixation**.
- Most myelomas have more than 6 mg/dL of urine Bence Jones protein; light chains in the urine and/or increased immunoglobulin in the blood are found in 99% of patients.
- About 20% of patients have only free light chains.

**Significance**

- Bence Jones proteins are excreted by the kidney and contribute to **myeloma kidney**.
- They are the most important factor in the renal failure seen in up to 50% of patients, being toxic to renal tubular epithelial cells.
- Certain light chains (lambda 6 and lambda 3 families) cause AL amyloidosis.

## What do you mean by sub-leukaemic leukaemia (marrow picture)?

**Subleukaemic leukaemia** is a leukaemia with abnormal white cells in the peripheral blood in which the total white cell count is normal.

In **aleukaemic leukaemia** blasts are entirely absent from the blood, so a bone marrow examination is essential to exclude acute leukaemia in pancytopenic patients.

## How will you differentiate sub-leukaemic leukaemia from aplastic anemia?

| Feature | Subleukaemic or aleukaemic leukaemia | Aplastic anaemia |
|---|---|---|
| **Bone marrow** | Hypercellular, packed with blasts, generally 20% or more | Markedly hypocellular, largely devoid of haematopoietic cells; fat cells, fibrous stroma and scattered lymphocytes and plasma cells remain |
| **Marrow aspirate** | Cells yielded, showing blasts | Often yields little material (dry tap); aplasia best seen on biopsy |
| **Blood** | Abnormal white cells present with a normal total count; in aleukaemic leukaemia blasts are absent; pancytopenia may be present | Pancytopenia inevitably; reticulocytopenia; red cells usually slightly macrocytic and normochromic |
| **Diagnosis** | Bone marrow examination is essential to exclude acute leukaemia in pancytopenic patients | Rests on bone marrow biopsy |

## Write a short note on polycythaemia rubra vera.

**Definition:** polycythaemia vera (PCV) is a myeloproliferative neoplasm with increased marrow production of red cells, granulocytes and platelets (panmyelosis); the increase in red cells causes most of the symptoms.

**Pathogenesis**

- Activating mutations of the tyrosine kinase **JAK2**; more than 97% have the valine-to-phenylalanine substitution at residue 617.
- Progenitors have markedly decreased requirements for erythropoietin, so serum erythropoietin is **low**; in secondary polycythaemia it is high.
- Raised haematocrit increases blood viscosity and sludging; with thrombocytosis and abnormal platelet function this predisposes to thrombosis and bleeding.

**Morphology**

- Hypercellular marrow with some residual fat; subtle increase in red cell progenitors, with more granulocytic precursors and megakaryocytes; increased reticulin in about 10%.
- Blood: increased basophils and abnormally large platelets.
- Mild organomegaly from congestion.

**Clinical features and complications**

- Insidious onset in late middle age; plethora and cyanosis; headache, dizziness, hypertension.
- Intense pruritus and peptic ulceration, possibly from histamine released by basophils.
- Hyperuricaemia, with secondary gout in 5% to 10%.
- **Thrombosis** and **bleeding.**
- **Spent phase** with marrow fibrosis and extramedullary haematopoiesis in the spleen and liver; transformation to acute myeloid leukaemia in about 1%.
- Untreated, death from bleeding or thrombosis occurs within months of diagnosis.

## Diagnostic criteria of hypersplenism.

- **Splenomegaly.**
- **Anaemia, leucopenia and/or thrombocytopenia**, singly or together.
- **Increased sequestration** of formed elements in the spleen, with enhanced phagocytosis by splenic macrophages, is the probable cause of the cytopenias.
- **Bone marrow:** with excessive peripheral destruction of cells the marrow is usually hypercellular, from a compensatory increase in precursors.

## Elderly man (55 or 65 years) with bone/back pain, punched-out skull lesions or osteolytic lesions, high ESR, vertebral fracture — Most probable diagnosis? How will you establish / confirm the diagnosis?

**Most probable diagnosis: multiple myeloma.**

**Confirmation**

1. Serum and urine electrophoresis for an M protein spike, characterised by immunofixation; Bence Jones protein [free monoclonal light chains in the urine] in the urine.
2. Bone marrow examination showing clonal plasma cells, usually more than 30% of the cellularity.
3. CRAB criteria: hypercalcaemia, renal dysfunction, anaemia and bone lesions.
4. Radiography showing punched-out lytic lesions.

## 40-year-old man with massive splenomegaly and total WBC count 2,50,000/cu.mm — Diagnosis?

**Diagnosis: chronic myeloid leukaemia (CML).**

- Massive splenomegaly from extramedullary haematopoiesis, with a white cell count above 100,000 per microlitre, in an adult.
- Blood: neutrophils, band forms, metamyelocytes, myelocytes, eosinophils and basophils; blasts usually less than 10%; platelets often increased.
- Confirmed by detection of the **BCR::ABL fusion gene** (Philadelphia chromosome [t(9;22) producing the BCR-ABL1 fusion], t(9;22)) by chromosomal analysis or PCR-based tests; this separates CML from other myeloproliferative neoplasms.

## 10-year-old boy with weakness, fever, joint pain and gum bleeding — Clinical diagnosis? Expected PBF findings?

**Clinical diagnosis: acute leukaemia**, most probably acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (ALL), the most common cancer of children.

- Weakness from anaemia; fever from infections secondary to neutropenia; gum bleeding from thrombocytopenia; joint and bone pain from marrow expansion and infiltration of the subperiosteum.

**Expected peripheral blood film findings**

- Anaemia, neutropenia and thrombocytopenia.
- Blast count highly variable; blasts may be absent from the blood (aleukaemic leukaemia).
- Lymphoblasts have condensed chromatin, small nucleoli and scant agranular cytoplasm.

Bone marrow examination is essential to confirm the diagnosis.

## What is haemopoiesis?

All formed elements of blood (red cells, granulocytes, monocytes, platelets and lymphocytes) have a common origin from **haematopoietic stem cells (HSCs)**, pluripotent cells at the apex of a hierarchy of bone marrow progenitors.

- HSCs have **pluripotency** (generating all mature blood cells) and **self-renewal**.
- Progressive commitment gives early progenitors, then colony-forming units, then morphologically recognisable precursors such as myeloblasts, proerythroblasts and megakaryoblasts.
- Short-term needs are regulated by **colony-stimulating factors** (erythropoietin, GM-CSF, G-CSF, thrombopoietin) acting on committed progenitors.

## Sites of normal haemopoiesis.

| Period | Site |
|---|---|
| **Third week of embryonic life** | Yolk sac; transient, mainly embryonic red cells |
| **Several weeks later** | Definitive HSCs arise in the mesoderm of the intraembryonic aorta/gonad/mesonephros region |
| **Third month** | Liver, the chief site until shortly before birth; HSCs also in the foetal placenta |
| **Fourth month onwards** | Bone marrow |
| **Birth** | Marrow throughout the skeleton is active; hepatic haematopoiesis dwindles |
| **After puberty** | Restricted to the axial skeleton; in adults only about half of the marrow space is active |

## Where does extramedullary haemopoiesis occur?

Extramedullary haematopoiesis occurs in the **spleen, liver and lymph nodes**, when circulating neoplastic or stressed stem cells settle there.

- **Chronic myeloid leukaemia:** spleen greatly enlarged from extramedullary haematopoiesis; mild hepatomegaly and lymphadenopathy.
- **Polycythaemia vera, spent phase:** spleen and liver, with prominent organomegaly.
- **Primary myelofibrosis:** spleen, liver and lymph nodes, as marrow fibrosis displaces haematopoiesis.
- **Severe beta-thalassaemia:** liver, spleen and lymph nodes; in extreme cases extraosseous masses in the thorax, abdomen and pelvis.

## Write the developmental stages of RBC.

1. **Haemopoietic stem cell:** pluripotent cell at the apex of the marrow hierarchy.
2. Committed erythroid progenitor (CFU-E, colony-forming unit erythroid): proliferates under erythropoietin.
3. **Proerythroblast:** the first morphologically recognisable red cell precursor.
4. **Basophilic erythroblast.**
5. **Polychromatic erythroblast.**
6. **Orthochromatic erythroblast (normoblast):** enucleates to form the reticulocyte.
7. **Reticulocyte:** released into the peripheral blood; larger than a mature red cell, with blue-red polychromatophilic cytoplasm due to RNA.
8. **Mature red cell (erythrocyte).**


## What are the essential nutrients for haemopoiesis?

1. **Iron:** needed for haemoglobin synthesis; deficiency leads to inadequate haemoglobin production and hypochromic microcytic anaemia.
2. **Vitamin B12:** needed for DNA synthesis; deficiency leads to megaloblastic anaemia.
3. **Folic acid:** needed for DNA synthesis; deficiency leads to megaloblastic anaemia.


## What is reticulocyte?

Reticulocytes are the newly released red cell precursors in the peripheral blood. They are larger than normal red cells and have blue-red **polychromatophilic** cytoplasm due to the presence of **RNA**, a feature that allows them to be identified in the clinical laboratory.


## Mention the cells in the steps of granulopoiesis.

1. **Myeloblast**
2. **Promyelocyte:** coarse primary (azurophilic) granules.
3. **Myelocyte:** specific (secondary) granules appear; neutrophilic, eosinophilic or basophilic.
4. **Metamyelocyte:** indented nucleus.
5. **Band form:** band-shaped nucleus.
6. **Segmented granulocyte:** mature cell with a lobed nucleus.


## Information you can get from a stained peripheral blood film.

- **Red cell size, colour and shape:** normocytic, microcytic or macrocytic; normochromic or hypochromic; red cell shape is assessed by visual inspection of the smear.
- Examples of shape: spherocytes in hereditary spherocytosis, sickled cells in sickle cell anaemia, macro-ovalocytes in megaloblastic anaemia, hypochromic microcytic cells in iron deficiency.
- **Reticulocytes:** larger, with blue-red polychromatophilic cytoplasm from RNA.
- **Rouleaux formation:** red cells stuck in linear arrays, from high M protein.
- **White cells:** toxic granulation, Döhle bodies [pale blue cytoplasmic inclusions in neutrophils] and vacuoles in sepsis; immature granulocytes (leukaemoid reaction, chronic myeloid leukaemia); blasts in acute leukaemia; increased basophils.
- **Platelets:** abnormally large platelets in polycythaemia vera.

## What are red cell indices?

Red cell indices describe the red cells measured by special instrumentation.

| Index | Meaning | Adult reference range |
|---|---|---|
| **Mean cell volume (MCV)** | Average volume of a red cell, in femtolitres | 82 to 96 fL |
| **Mean cell haemoglobin (MCH)** | Average haemoglobin content (mass) per red cell, in picograms | 27 to 33 pg |
| **Mean cell haemoglobin concentration (MCHC)** | Average concentration of haemoglobin in a given volume of packed red cells, in g/dL | 33 to 37 g/dL |
| **Red cell distribution width (RDW)** | Coefficient of variation of red cell volume | 11.5 to 14.5 |

## Importance of red cell indices.

- They are the most useful measurements in the **morphological classification of anaemia**, giving clues to the cause.
- **MCV** classifies red cells as normocytic, microcytic or macrocytic; **MCH and MCHC** reflect haemoglobinisation, normochromic or hypochromic.
- **Microcytic hypochromic anaemias** are caused by disorders of haemoglobin synthesis, for example iron deficiency and thalassaemia.
- **Macrocytic anaemias** often stem from abnormalities that impair maturation of erythroid precursors in the marrow, for example megaloblastic anaemia.
- **Normochromic normocytic anaemias** have diverse causes.
- **RDW** reflects variation in red cell size (anisocytosis).

## Normal haemoglobin level in different age groups / influence of age and sex on Hb estimation.

| Group | Haemoglobin (g/dL) |
|---|---|
| **Adult male** | 13.6 to 17.2 |
| **Adult female** | 12.0 to 15.0 |

- **Anaemia is usually diagnosed** from reductions in haematocrit and haemoglobin concentration below the normal range.
- **Haemoglobin correlates** with red cell mass except when plasma volume changes, as in fluid retention or dehydration.

## What information can you get from a stained PBF?

**Red cells**

- **Size:** normocytic, microcytic or macrocytic; variation in size (anisocytosis).
- **Haemoglobinisation:** normochromic or hypochromic; the zone of central pallor is enlarged in iron deficiency.
- **Shape (poikilocytosis):** pencil cells in iron deficiency; target cells, basophilic stippling and fragmented cells in thalassaemia; macro-ovalocytes in megaloblastic anaemia; sickled cells; spherocytes.
- **Reticulocytes:** larger, with blue-red polychromatophilic cytoplasm because of RNA.
- **Nucleated red cell precursors** (normoblasts), in severe anaemia and thalassaemia.

**White cells:** hypersegmented neutrophils with five or more nuclear lobules in megaloblastic anaemia.

**Platelets:** large platelets (megathrombocytes) in immune thrombocytopenic purpura; confirms a low count, since clumping of platelets during automated counting can cause spurious thrombocytopenia.


## Define ESR / write a short note on ESR.

**Definition:** the erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) is the rate at which red cells settle in a vertical tube of anticoagulated blood over one hour, measured in millimetres in the first hour.

**Methods:** Westergren [most commonly used], modified Westergren, Wintrobe [ESR method using a 100 mm tube] and micro-ESR.

**Principle**

- Red cells normally repel each other because of the negative charge on their surfaces and settle slowly.
- Fibrinogen and immunoglobulins neutralise this repulsion and make the red cells aggregate into stacks called **rouleaux**, which are heavier and sediment faster.
- ESR depends on the balance between factors that promote and factors that resist sedimentation.

**Phases of sedimentation:** rouleaux formation, rapid sedimentation at a constant rate, and packing of the rouleaux with slow sedimentation.

**Technical requirements:** the tube must be held upright; tilting and higher temperature increase the rate; the sample should be set up within two hours of collection.

**Clinical significance**

- A nonspecific test, a general index of disease activity, interpreted with the clinical findings.
- Raised in infectious, inflammatory and malignant conditions that change plasma proteins; also raised in anaemia and pregnancy.
- Low in polycythaemia, sickle cell anaemia and hereditary spherocytosis.
- A normal or mildly raised ESR does not exclude significant disease.


## Mention the major physiological factors affecting ESR.

1. **Age:** values increase slightly with age.
2. **Sex.**
3. **Pregnancy:** values rise, because of haemodilution and an increased fibrinogen to albumin ratio, which increases rouleaux formation.
4. **Lifestyle:** obesity, smoking and physical activity.


## How do changes in blood components influence ESR?

**Plasma proteins**

- **Increased fibrinogen** and immunoglobulins promote rouleaux formation and increase ESR.
- **Albumin** retards sedimentation; hypoalbuminaemia permits faster sedimentation.
- Removal of fibrinogen by defibrination decreases ESR.

**Red cells**

- **Anaemia** increases ESR.
- **Polycythaemia** decreases ESR.
- **Sickle cells and spherocytes** cannot form rouleaux, so ESR is low in sickle cell anaemia and hereditary spherocytosis.
- **Macrocytes** settle faster; microcytes settle more slowly.


## What are the iron profile tests?

| Test | Finding in iron deficiency |
|---|---|
| **Serum iron** | Low |
| **Serum ferritin** | Low; below 12 µg/L |
| **Total iron-binding capacity** (reflects transferrin) | High |
| **Transferrin saturation** | Below 15% |
| **Serum hepcidin** | Low, because reduced iron stores inhibit its synthesis |

In health transferrin is about one-third saturated; serum iron averages 120 µg/dL in males and 100 µg/dL in females.


## Enumerate the laboratory methods of assessing iron stores.

1. **Serum ferritin:** plasma ferritin generally correlates with body iron stores; below 12 µg/L in iron deficiency; values approaching 5000 µg/L in iron overload.
2. **Bone marrow iron:** Prussian blue stain on smears of aspirated marrow; absence of stainable iron in macrophages is diagnostic of iron deficiency, and increased storage iron is seen in anaemia of chronic inflammation.
3. **Serum iron and transferrin saturation:** fall as the storage pool is depleted, before anaemia appears.


## Define PCV.

The **packed cell volume** (**haematocrit**) is the ratio of packed red cells to total blood volume.


## Name the red cell (absolute) indices with values.

| Index | Definition | Adult reference range |
|---|---|---|
| **Mean cell volume (MCV)** | Average volume of a red cell | 82 to 96 fL |
| **Mean cell haemoglobin (MCH)** | Average content (mass) of haemoglobin per red cell | 27 to 33 pg |
| **Mean cell haemoglobin concentration (MCHC)** | Average concentration of haemoglobin in a given volume of packed red cells | 33 to 37 g/dL |
| **Red cell distribution width (RDW)** | Coefficient of variation of red cell volume | 11.5 to 14.5 |


## Write a short note on MCV.

**Definition:** the mean cell volume is the average volume of a red cell, expressed in femtolitres (fL).

**Reference range:** 82 to 96 fL.

**Use:** it classifies anaemia by red cell size.

- **Microcytic** (low MCV): microcytic hypochromic anaemias are caused by disorders of haemoglobin synthesis, such as iron deficiency anaemia and thalassaemia.
- **Normocytic:** for example acute blood loss.
- **Macrocytic** (high MCV): macrocytic anaemias often stem from abnormalities that impair the maturation of erythroid precursors in the bone marrow, such as megaloblastic anaemia of vitamin B12 or folate deficiency.


## Indications of bone marrow biopsy / aspiration / examination.

- **Leukaemias:** acute leukaemias in adults; essential to exclude acute leukaemia, aleukaemic leukaemia and myelodysplastic neoplasm in pancytopenic patients.
- **Multiple myeloma** and **Waldenström macroglobulinaemia** [lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma with IgM paraprotein]: definitive diagnosis of myeloma requires bone marrow examination.
- **Bone marrow metastases** and some storage diseases.
- **Aplastic anaemia:** diagnosis rests on bone marrow biopsy.
- Agranulocytosis, immune thrombocytopenic purpura and hypersplenism.
- **Staging of Hodgkin lymphoma** [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] (bone marrow biopsy).
- **Trephine biopsy** is added when no marrow is aspirated despite repeated attempts (dry tap or blood tap), in bone marrow necrosis, metabolic bone disease and when looking for metastases.

## How is trephine biopsy done?

- **Trephine biopsy** takes a core of bone marrow with a long hollow needle; a guidewire pushes the specimen out from the sharp end, which avoids crushing the marrow.
- The usual site in adults is the **posterior iliac crest**; aspiration is usually from the sternum or iliac crest.
- Aspiration and trephine biopsy are complementary and together give a higher diagnostic yield.
- It is used when aspiration fails (dry tap or blood tap); aplasia, for example, is best appreciated in marrow biopsies.

## What do you mean by blood tap and dry tap?

**Dry tap or blood tap:** failure to obtain marrow particles on bone marrow aspiration; reported in 2.7% to 10% of aspirates.

- It is a red flag for underlying marrow pathology rather than only a failed procedure; in one series 93.7% had an underlying bone marrow pathology and 6.3% a faulty technique.
- Causes include acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (commonest in children), non-Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] and metastatic tumours (commonest in the elderly), marrow fibrosis, and aplastic anaemia.
- **The trephine biopsy must always be studied** in such cases.

## How can bone marrow be examined?

Bone marrow is examined by two procedures, usually done together:

1. **Bone marrow aspiration:** a sample of semi-liquid marrow is withdrawn through a needle.
2. **Trephine biopsy:** a core of bone with solid marrow is removed with a different, wider needle (Jamshidi needle), usually from the posterior iliac crest.


## What are the indications of bone marrow examination/aspiration?

- **Leukaemias:** acute leukaemias in adults; to exclude acute leukaemia, aleukaemic leukaemia and myelodysplastic neoplasm in pancytopenic patients.
- **Multiple myeloma** and **Waldenström macroglobulinaemia** [lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma with IgM paraprotein]: definitive diagnosis of myeloma requires bone marrow examination.
- **Bone marrow metastases** and some storage diseases.
- **Aplastic anaemia:** diagnosis rests on bone marrow biopsy.
- Agranulocytosis, immune thrombocytopenic purpura and hypersplenism.
- **Staging of Hodgkin lymphoma** [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] (bone marrow biopsy).
- **Trephine biopsy** is added when no marrow is aspirated despite repeated attempts (dry tap or blood tap), in bone marrow necrosis, metabolic bone disease and when looking for metastases.


## Name the white cell disorders for which bone marrow aspiration is done.

1. **Acute leukaemias**.
2. **Aleukaemic leukaemia**.
3. **Myelodysplastic neoplasm**.
4. **Multiple myeloma and** Waldenström macroglobulinaemia [lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma with IgM paraprotein].
5. **Agranulocytosis**.
6. **Staging of Hodgkin lymphoma** [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] (bone marrow biopsy).


## Mention the site of marrow puncture in a child below 2 years.

The **anteromedial face of the tibia** (upper end of the shinbone). In children younger than 18 months it is the preferred site for marrow aspiration.


## What is trephine biopsy?

Trephine biopsy is a procedure in which a **core of bone marrow** is taken with a trephine needle (Jamshidi needle), usually from the **posterior superior iliac crest**. The core is processed to evaluate marrow cellularity and to rule out marrow involvement by solid tumours, lymphomas or other processes.

Indications:

- Inadequate or failed marrow aspiration.
- Suspected bone marrow fibrosis.


## What is dry tap?

**Dry tap** is the failure to obtain marrow particles on bone marrow aspiration.

- It is a red flag for underlying marrow pathology and not only a failed procedure.
- The trephine biopsy must always be studied in such cases.


## What is blood tap?

A **blood tap** is an aspirate that yields blood without marrow particles. It is grouped with dry tap as a failed aspiration, and the trephine biopsy must be studied.


## Mention 4 conditions where dry tap is frequent.

1. **Marrow fibrosis** (myelofibrosis).
2. **Aplastic anaemia.**
3. **Acute lymphoblastic leukaemia** (commonest in children).
4. **Metastatic tumours** (commonest in the elderly).
5. **Non-Hodgkin lymphoma.**


## What is anemia?

**Anaemia** is a reduction of the total circulating red cell mass below normal limits.

## Morphological types of anaemia.

1. **Microcytic hypochromic:** red cells small and with reduced haemoglobin.
2. **Normocytic normochromic.**
3. **Macrocytic:** red cells large.

Classification rests on red cell size (normocytic, microcytic, macrocytic), degree of haemoglobinisation shown by colour (normochromic, hypochromic), and shape.

## Examples of each morphological type.

| Morphological type | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Microcytic hypochromic** | Iron deficiency anaemia; thalassaemia |
| **Macrocytic** | Megaloblastic anaemia from vitamin B12 and folate deficiency |
