| **Normocytic normochromic** | Acute blood loss; anaemia of chronic inflammation, whose cells may be normocytic and normochromic or slightly hypochromic and microcytic |

## Pathophysiological categories of anaemia.

1. **Blood loss**
   - Acute: trauma.
   - Chronic: gastrointestinal lesions, gynaecological disturbances.
2. **Increased red cell destruction (haemolysis)**
   - Inherited: membrane disorders, enzyme deficiencies, haemoglobin abnormalities.
   - Acquired: paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria, antibody-mediated, mechanical trauma, infections of red cells, toxic injury, membrane lipid abnormalities, sequestration (hypersplenism).
3. **Decreased red cell production**
   - Inherited defects, nutritional deficiencies, erythropoietin deficiency, immune-mediated injury of progenitors, inflammation-mediated iron sequestration, primary haematopoietic neoplasms, space-occupying marrow lesions, infections of red cell progenitors, unknown mechanisms.

## Causes of anaemia.

| Mechanism | Specific examples |
|---|---|
| **Acute blood loss** | Trauma |
| **Chronic blood loss** | Gastrointestinal tract lesions, gynaecological disturbances |
| **Red cell membrane disorders** | Hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis |
| **Enzyme deficiencies** | G6PD deficiency, glutathione synthetase deficiency, pyruvate kinase deficiency, hexokinase deficiency |
| **Haemoglobin abnormalities** | Thalassaemia syndromes; sickle cell disease, unstable haemoglobins |
| **Acquired defects** | Paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria |
| **Antibody-mediated destruction** | Haemolytic disease of the newborn, transfusion reactions, drug-induced, autoimmune disorders |
| **Mechanical trauma** | Microangiopathic haemolytic anaemias (haemolytic uraemic syndrome, disseminated intravascular coagulation, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura); defective cardiac valves; repetitive physical trauma |
| **Infections of red cells** | Malaria, babesiosis |
| **Toxic or chemical injury** | Clostridial sepsis, snake venom, lead poisoning |
| **Membrane lipid abnormalities** | Abetalipoproteinaemia, severe hepatocellular liver disease |
| **Sequestration** | Hypersplenism |
| **Stem cell depletion** | Fanconi anaemia [inherited DNA-repair defect causing marrow failure], telomerase defects |
| **Nutritional deficiencies** | Vitamin B12 and folate deficiency; iron deficiency |
| **Erythropoietin deficiency** | Renal failure, anaemia of chronic inflammation |
| **Immune-mediated injury of progenitors** | Aplastic anaemia, pure red cell aplasia |
| **Primary haematopoietic neoplasms** | Acute leukaemia, myelodysplastic syndrome, myeloproliferative neoplasms |
| **Space-occupying marrow lesions** | Metastatic neoplasms, granulomatous disease |
| Infections of red cell progenitors | Parvovirus B19 infection |
| **Unknown mechanisms** | Endocrine disorders, hepatocellular liver disease |

## How will you investigate a case of anaemia?

1. **Haemoglobin and haematocrit:** anaemia is usually diagnosed from reductions in these below the normal range (male 13.6 to 17.2 g/dL, female 12.0 to 15.0 g/dL).
2. **Red cell indices:** mean cell volume, mean cell haemoglobin, mean cell haemoglobin concentration and red cell distribution width, to classify the anaemia as microcytic, normocytic or macrocytic, and hypochromic or normochromic.
3. **Peripheral blood smear:** red cell shape, and other findings that point to a cause.
4. **Reticulocyte count:** raised after blood loss, reaching 10% to 15% after 7 days; reticulocytopenia in aplastic anaemia.
5. **Iron studies** in suspected iron deficiency: serum iron and ferritin low, total iron-binding capacity high, transferrin saturation below 15%.
6. **Bone marrow examination:** aspirate with Prussian blue stain for iron; biopsy for aplasia.
7. **Search for the underlying cause**, for example gastrointestinal blood loss in adult males and postmenopausal women.

## Write the morphological/red-cell-indices classification of anemia with examples.

Anaemia is classified by red cell size (normocytic, microcytic, macrocytic), degree of haemoglobinisation shown by colour (normochromic, hypochromic) and shape.

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Microcytic hypochromic** (disorders of haemoglobin synthesis) | Iron deficiency anaemia; thalassaemia syndromes |
| **Normocytic normochromic** (diverse aetiologies) | Acute blood loss; mild anaemia of hypothyroidism; anaemia of chronic inflammation (may also be slightly hypochromic and microcytic) |
| **Macrocytic** (impaired maturation of erythroid precursors) | Megaloblastic anaemia of vitamin B12 or folate deficiency; aplastic anaemia (slightly macrocytic and normochromic) |


## Write a short note on investigations for morphological classification of anemia.

1. **Haemoglobin concentration and haematocrit:** reductions below the normal range establish anaemia.
2. **Red cell indices:** mean cell volume, mean cell haemoglobin, mean cell haemoglobin concentration and red cell distribution width, determined by instrumentation; they classify size and haemoglobinisation.
3. **Peripheral blood smear:** visual inspection for red cell shape and other morphology that points to specific causes.
4. **Reticulocyte count:** reference range 0.5% to 1.5%.


## Define polycythemia.

**Polycythaemia** denotes an abnormally high number of circulating red cells, usually with a corresponding increase in the haemoglobin level. It may be relative (haemoconcentration from decreased plasma volume) or absolute (an increase in the total red cell mass).


## Causes of iron deficiency anaemia.

Iron deficiency can result from:

1. **Dietary lack**, in infants, the impoverished, older adults and vegans.
2. **Impaired absorption:** sprue, other causes of fat malabsorption (steatorrhoea), chronic diarrhoea, and gastrectomy.
3. **Increased requirement:** growing infants, children and adolescents, and premenopausal females, particularly during pregnancy.
4. **Chronic blood loss:** external haemorrhage, or bleeding into the gastrointestinal, urinary or genital tracts; the most common cause in developed societies.

## Causes of IDA in relation to different age groups.

| Group | Causes |
|---|---|
| **Infants** | Dietary lack, as milk contains very little iron; increased requirement for growth |
| **Children and adolescents** | Increased requirement for growth |
| **Premenopausal females** | Increased requirement, particularly during pregnancy; chronic blood loss from the genital tract; multiple, closely spaced pregnancies in low-resource countries carry exceptional risk |
| **Adult males** | Chronic blood loss, attributed to gastrointestinal bleeding until proven otherwise |
| **Postmenopausal females** | Chronic blood loss, attributed to gastrointestinal bleeding until proven otherwise |
| **Older adults** | Restricted diets with little meat, from limited income or poor dentition |
| **Any age** | Poor diet from poverty; vegan diet; malabsorption; gastrectomy |

## Causes of IDA in adult males.

- **Chronic gastrointestinal blood loss.** Iron deficiency in adult males in high-income countries must be attributed to gastrointestinal blood loss until proven otherwise.
- Ascribing it prematurely to another cause risks missing a gastrointestinal cancer or other bleeding lesion.
- Other causes: bleeding into the urinary tract, malabsorption, and a restricted or vegan diet.

## Causes of IDA in post-menopausal women.

- **Chronic gastrointestinal blood loss.** Iron deficiency in postmenopausal females in high-income countries must be attributed to gastrointestinal blood loss until proven otherwise.
- Ascribing it prematurely to another cause risks missing a gastrointestinal cancer or other bleeding lesion.
- Other causes: malabsorption, and a restricted diet with little meat in older adults.

## Causes of IDA in infants and children.

- **Dietary lack:** infants are at high risk because milk contains very little iron. Human breast milk provides only about 0.3 mg/L; cow's milk contains about twice as much, but its bioavailability is poor.
- **Increased requirement** in growing infants, children and adolescents.
- **Impaired absorption:** sprue, other causes of fat malabsorption (steatorrhoea) and chronic diarrhoea.
- **Chronic blood loss:** external haemorrhage, or bleeding into the gastrointestinal, urinary or genital tracts.

## Important laboratory findings in IDA.

- **Haemoglobin and haematocrit** depressed, usually to a moderate degree.
- **Blood film:** hypochromia, microcytosis and modest poikilocytosis; small elongated "pencil" cells; enlarged central pallor, with haemoglobin only in a narrow peripheral rim.
- **Serum iron and ferritin low.**
- **Total plasma iron-binding capacity high**, reflecting elevated transferrin.
- **Transferrin saturation** below 15%.
- **Hepcidin** serum levels fall.
- **Bone marrow:** mild to moderate increase in erythroid progenitors; absence of stainable iron in macrophages on Prussian blue stain of aspirate.
- **Response to iron:** oral iron raises reticulocytes in about 5 to 7 days, followed by a steady rise in counts and normalisation of red cell indices.

## Biochemical tests done for IDA.

- **Serum iron:** low.
- **Serum ferritin:** low.
- **Total iron-binding capacity:** high.
- **Transferrin saturation:** below 15%.
- **Serum hepcidin:** low, as reduced iron stores inhibit its synthesis.
- **Stainable marrow iron** (Prussian blue stain of aspirated marrow): absent in macrophages.
- Progressive depletion of stores first lowers serum iron and transferrin saturation without anaemia; anaemia appears only when stores are completely depleted, with low serum iron and ferritin.

## Who are more susceptible to IDA, and why?

- **Infants:** very little iron in milk, and growing requirement.
- **Children and adolescents:** increased requirement for growth.
- **Premenopausal females:** increased requirement, particularly in pregnancy; females in low-resource countries with multiple, closely spaced pregnancies are at exceptional risk. Many females consume only marginal amounts of iron, while most males ingest more than adequate amounts.
- **The impoverished:** suboptimal diets at any age.
- **Older adults:** restricted diets with little meat, from limited income or poor dentition.
- **Vegans:** no haem iron in the diet.
- **Patients with malabsorption or gastrectomy:** gastrectomy lowers duodenal acidity and speeds transit through the duodenum.
- **Adult males and postmenopausal females:** chronic gastrointestinal blood loss.

## Why is the prognosis of aplastic anaemia worse than IDA?

| Feature | Aplastic anaemia | Iron deficiency anaemia |
|---|---|---|
| **Nature** | Markedly hypocellular marrow, largely devoid of haematopoietic cells; pancytopenia inevitably appears | Inadequate haemoglobin production from lack of iron |
| **Consequences** | Anaemia, petechiae and ecchymoses, and frequent, persistent infections or sudden chills, fever and prostration | Hypochromic microcytic anaemia; symptoms often those of the underlying cause |
| **Treatment** | Stem cell transplantation, with 5-year survival of more than 75% where a donor exists; immunosuppressive therapy for older patients or those without a donor | Oral iron produces a reticulocyte rise in 5 to 7 days and normalisation of red cell indices |
| **Prognosis** | Variable | Correctable once the cause is treated |

## Differential diagnosis of microcytic hypochromic anaemia.

| Cause | Features |
|---|---|
| **Iron deficiency anaemia** | Low serum iron and ferritin, high iron-binding capacity, transferrin saturation below 15%; no stainable marrow iron |
| **Thalassaemia** | Hypochromia, microcytosis, basophilic stippling and target cells; in trait, haemoglobin A2 increased to 4% to 8% |
| **Anaemia of chronic inflammation** | Red cells normocytic and normochromic, or slightly hypochromic and microcytic; low serum iron, reduced iron-binding capacity, high ferritin |
| **TMPRSS6 mutation** (rare) | High hepcidin, reduced iron absorption, failure to respond to iron therapy |

## How will you differentiate the causes of microcytic hypochromic anaemia?

| Test | Iron deficiency | Anaemia of chronic inflammation | Thalassaemia trait |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Serum iron** | Low | Low | Not stated |
| **Total iron-binding capacity** | High | Reduced | Not stated |
| **Serum ferritin** | Low | High | Not stated |
| **Marrow iron in macrophages** | Absent | Increased | Not stated |
| **Haemoglobin electrophoresis** | Not stated | Not stated | Haemoglobin A2 increased to 4% to 8% |
| **Blood film** | Pencil cells | Normocytic or slightly hypochromic | Target cells, basophilic stippling |

## Name the changes of absolute values in iron deficiency anemia.

Iron deficiency anaemia gives a microcytic hypochromic anaemia:

1. **Mean cell volume:** reduced.
2. **Mean cell haemoglobin:** reduced.
3. **Mean cell haemoglobin concentration:** reduced.

Haemoglobin and haematocrit are depressed. Oral iron normalises the red cell indices.


## What are the causes of microcytic hypochromic anemia?

Microcytic hypochromic anaemias are caused by disorders of haemoglobin synthesis.

1. **Iron deficiency anaemia**, the commonest cause.
2. **Thalassaemia syndromes.**
3. **Anaemia of chronic inflammation:** the red cells may be normocytic and normochromic, or slightly hypochromic and microcytic.
4. **TMPRSS6 mutations** (rare): high hepcidin, reduced iron absorption and failure to respond to iron therapy.


## How can the causes of microcytic anemia be differentiated by laboratory investigation?

| Feature | Iron deficiency | Anaemia of chronic inflammation | Thalassaemia trait |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Serum iron** | Low | Low | Iron deficiency excluded by serum iron, iron-binding capacity and ferritin |
| **Total iron-binding capacity** | High | Reduced | |
| **Serum ferritin** | Low | High | |
| **Transferrin saturation** | Below 15% | | |
| **Marrow macrophage iron** | Absent | Increased | |
| **Haemoglobin electrophoresis** | | | HbA2 increased to 4% to 8% of total haemoglobin; HbF normal or slightly increased |
| **Blood film** | Hypochromia, microcytosis, pencil cells | Normocytic normochromic, or slightly hypochromic and microcytic | Hypochromia, microcytosis, basophilic stippling, target cells |


## Enumerate the causes of iron deficiency anemia.

1. **Dietary lack:** infants, the impoverished, older adults and vegans (lack of haem iron).
2. **Impaired absorption:** sprue, other causes of fat malabsorption (steatorrhoea), chronic diarrhoea, and gastrectomy (decreased acidity of the proximal duodenum and faster passage of gut contents).
3. **Increased requirement:** growing infants, children and adolescents, and premenopausal females, particularly during pregnancy; multiple, closely spaced pregnancies carry exceptionally high risk.
4. **Chronic blood loss:** external haemorrhage or bleeding into the gastrointestinal, urinary or genital tracts; the commonest cause in developed societies. In adult males and postmenopausal females, gastrointestinal blood loss must be assumed until proven otherwise.


## Why is iron deficiency the most common anemia in infancy and childhood?

1. **Very little iron in milk:** human breast milk provides only about 0.3 mg/L; cow's milk contains about twice as much, but its bioavailability is poor.
2. **Increased requirement** in growing infants, children and adolescents.


## Give the changes in iron deficiency anemia.

**Stages**

1. Progressive depletion of iron stores first lowers serum iron and transferrin saturation without anaemia; marrow erythroid activity is increased.
2. Anaemia appears only when stores are completely depleted, with low serum iron and ferritin and low transferrin saturation.

**Bone marrow:** mild to moderate increase in erythroid progenitors; absence of stainable iron in macrophages.

**Peripheral blood:** small (microcytic) and pale (hypochromic) red cells; enlarged zone of central pallor; pencil cells.

**Tissue changes in severe, long-standing deficiency:** koilonychia, alopecia, atrophic changes in the tongue and gastric mucosa, and intestinal malabsorption; pica, a craving for non-foodstuffs such as clay, or for ice (pagophagia). Oesophageal web with microcytic hypochromic anaemia and atrophic glossitis forms the triad of Plummer-Vinson syndrome [oesophageal web with iron deficiency anaemia].


## Give the laboratory diagnosis/blood picture of iron deficiency anemia.

**Blood picture**

- Haemoglobin and haematocrit depressed, usually to a moderate degree.
- Hypochromia, microcytosis and modest poikilocytosis; pencil cells.

**Iron studies**

- Serum iron low.
- Serum ferritin low.
- Total iron-binding capacity high.
- Transferrin saturation below 15%.
- Serum hepcidin low.

**Bone marrow:** mild to moderate increase in erythroid progenitors; absence of stainable iron in macrophages on Prussian blue stain of aspirated marrow.

**Response to iron:** oral iron produces a rise in reticulocytes in about 5 to 7 days, followed by a steady increase in blood counts and normalisation of red cell indices.


## A pregnant lady has weakness, exertional dyspnoea, pale conjunctivae, smooth tongue. What is your probable diagnosis?

**Probable diagnosis:** iron deficiency anaemia.

- Increased requirement in pregnancy.
- Weakness, malaise and dyspnoea on mild exertion are the general symptoms of anaemia.
- Smooth tongue: atrophic changes in the tongue from depletion of iron-containing enzymes.


## A 50y male with bleeding peptic ulcer has Hb 9 g%. What morphological type of anemia do you expect on blood film?

**Microcytic hypochromic anaemia** of iron deficiency.

- Chronic blood loss, here gastrointestinal, depletes iron reserves and leads to iron deficiency anaemia.
- Red cells are small and pale, with an enlarged zone of central pallor and pencil cells.


## Same patient: what will be his iron profile?

1. **Serum iron low**.
2. **Serum ferritin low**.
3. **Total iron-binding capacity high**.
4. **Transferrin saturation below 15%**.
5. **Absent stainable iron** in marrow macrophages.


## Define macrocytic anemia.

**Macrocytic anaemia** is an anaemia in which the red cells are larger than normal, the adult reference range for mean cell volume being 82 to 96 fL.

## Classify macrocytic anemia with example.

| Type | Example |
|---|---|
| **Megaloblastic** | Deficiency of vitamin B12 or folate, leading to inadequate thymidine synthesis and defective DNA replication; enlarged abnormal precursors (megaloblasts), ineffective haematopoiesis, macro-ovalocytes and, in most cases, pancytopenia |
| **Nonmegaloblastic** | Anaemia of hepatocellular disease, slightly macrocytic from lipid abnormalities that add phospholipid and cholesterol to red cell membranes |

## Causes of normoblastic macrocytic anaemia.

- **Hepatocellular disease**, toxic, infectious or cirrhotic: the anaemia is often slightly macrocytic because lipid abnormalities of liver failure make red cell membranes acquire phospholipid and cholesterol, increasing cell size.
- **Aplastic anaemia:** red cells usually slightly macrocytic and normochromic.
- **Reticulocytosis**, as after bleeding, since reticulocytes are larger than normal red cells.

## Write a short note on megaloblastic anemia.

**Definition**

The common theme among the various causes of megaloblastic anaemia is an impairment of DNA synthesis that leads to ineffective haematopoiesis and distinctive morphological changes, including abnormally large erythroid precursors and red cells.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- **Vitamin B12 and folic acid** are cofactors required for the synthesis of thymidine, one of the four bases found in DNA.
- A deficiency of these vitamins, or impairment of their metabolism, results in defective nuclear maturation due to deranged or inadequate DNA synthesis, with a delay or block in cell division.
- The two principal subtypes are **pernicious anaemia** (the major form of vitamin B12 deficiency anaemia) and **folate deficiency anaemia**.
- Other causes: ileal resection or disease, fish tapeworm infestation, bacterial overgrowth in blind loops, and metabolic inhibitors of DNA synthesis or folate metabolism such as **methotrexate**.
- The derangement in DNA synthesis causes most precursors to undergo **apoptosis** in the marrow (ineffective haematopoiesis) and leads to pancytopenia. A mild degree of red cell haemolysis of uncertain aetiology adds to the anaemia.

**Morphology**

- Blood: **macro-ovalocytes** (macrocytic and oval red cells) are highly characteristic; most lack central pallor and appear hyperchromic, but the mean cell haemoglobin concentration is not raised.
- Marked anisocytosis and poikilocytosis; **low reticulocyte count**; nucleated red cell progenitors occasionally appear when anaemia is severe.
- Neutrophils are larger than normal with **nuclear hypersegmentation**, five or more lobules instead of the normal three to four.
- Marrow: markedly hypercellular, with megaloblastic changes at all stages of erythroid development.
- Promegaloblasts are large, with deeply basophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli and fine nuclear chromatin; the nucleus keeps its fine chromatin instead of developing the clumped pyknotic chromatin of normoblasts, while cytoplasmic maturation proceeds normally (**nuclear-to-cytoplasmic asynchrony**).
- Granulocytic precursors show **giant metamyelocytes** and band forms; megakaryocytes may be abnormally large with bizarre multilobate nuclei.

**Clinical features and complications**

- Pernicious anaemia is insidious and the anaemia is often quite severe by the time it comes to medical attention.
- Vitamin B12 deficiency produces central nervous system lesions: demyelination of the dorsal and lateral spinal tracts, giving spastic paraparesis, sensory ataxia and severe paraesthesias of the lower limbs. Folate deficiency does not cause neurological changes.
- Folate given to a patient with vitamin B12 deficiency corrects the anaemia but does not prevent, and may worsen, the neurological deficit.

## How can you differentiate megaloblastic anaemia from other macrocytic anaemias?

| Feature | Megaloblastic anaemia | Aplastic anaemia (slightly macrocytic) |
|---|---|---|
| **Red cells** | Macro-ovalocytes; marked anisocytosis and poikilocytosis | Usually slightly macrocytic and normochromic |
| **Neutrophils** | Larger than normal, with five or more nuclear lobules (hypersegmentation) | Neutropenia |
| **Reticulocytes** | Low | Reticulocytopenia is the rule |
| **Marrow cellularity** | Markedly hypercellular | Markedly hypocellular, largely fat cells |
| **Marrow cells** | Megaloblasts with nuclear-to-cytoplasmic asynchrony; giant metamyelocytes; large megakaryocytes | Largely devoid of haematopoietic cells; "dry tap" on aspiration |
| **Cause** | Impaired DNA synthesis from vitamin B12 or folate deficiency | Chronic primary haematopoietic failure |

## How can you diagnose a case of megaloblastic anaemia?

1. **Peripheral blood:** macro-ovalocytes, marked anisocytosis and poikilocytosis, low reticulocyte count, and neutrophils with five or more nuclear lobules.
2. **Bone marrow:** markedly hypercellular, with megaloblasts at all stages, nuclear-to-cytoplasmic asynchrony, giant metamyelocytes and band forms, and large megakaryocytes with bizarre nuclei.
3. **Find the cause by the vitamin level:**
   - Vitamin B12 deficiency: low serum vitamin B12; raised serum homocysteine and methylmalonic acid; serum antibodies to intrinsic factor, which are highly specific for pernicious anaemia.
   - Folate deficiency: decreased serum or red cell folate; homocysteine raised but methylmalonate normal; no neurological changes.
4. **Response to treatment:** an outpouring of reticulocytes and a rise in haematocrit beginning about 5 days after parenteral vitamin B12 confirms vitamin B12 deficiency.
5. Vitamin B12 deficiency must be excluded before starting folate, because folate does not prevent the neurological deficit.

## Causes of folate deficiency.

The three major causes are decreased intake, increased requirements and impaired utilisation.

1. **Decreased intake**
   - Nutritionally inadequate diet, most frequently in chronic alcohol use disorder, the indigent and the very old; in alcohol use disorder with cirrhosis, trapping of folate in the liver, excessive urinary loss and disordered folate metabolism are also implicated.
   - Impaired intestinal absorption: malabsorption syndromes such as sprue, and infiltrative diseases of the small intestine such as lymphoma.
   - Drugs that interfere with absorption: the anticonvulsant **phenytoin** and oral contraceptives.
2. **Increased requirements:** pregnancy, infancy, hyperactive haematopoiesis (for example chronic haemolytic anaemia) and disseminated cancer.
3. **Impaired utilisation:** folic acid antagonists such as **methotrexate** inhibit dihydrofolate reductase and lead to a deficiency of tetrahydrofolic acid. Many chemotherapeutic drugs that damage DNA or inhibit DNA synthesis also cause megaloblastic changes.

## Why is folate deficiency more common than vitamin B12 deficiency?

**Folate**

- **The body's reserves** of folate are relatively modest, and a deficiency can arise within weeks to months if intake is inadequate.
- **Folic acid** in food is largely folylpolyglutamates, which are sensitive to heat; boiling, steaming or frying for 5 to 10 minutes destroys up to 95% of the folate content.
- **Requirements rise** in pregnancy, infancy, hyperactive haematopoiesis and disseminated cancer, so normal intake becomes inadequate.
- **Alcohol use disorder**, malabsorption, and drugs such as phenytoin, oral contraceptives and methotrexate all cause deficiency.

**Vitamin** B12

- **The daily requirement** is only 2 to 3 µg.
- **A diet** that includes animal products contains significantly more than the minimal daily requirement and builds intrahepatic stores sufficient to last several years.
- **Only strictly vegetarian** or macrobiotic diets, which contain little cobalamin, fail to provide enough.

## Causes of vitamin B12 deficiency.

1. **Decreased intake:** strictly vegetarian or macrobiotic diets, because plants and vegetables contain little cobalamin.
2. **Impaired absorption**
   - **Pernicious anaemia:** autoimmune gastritis impairing the production of intrinsic factor.
   - **Gastrectomy:** intrinsic factor is lost.
   - Achlorhydria and loss of pepsin secretion, in some older adults: vitamin B12 is not readily released from food proteins.
   - Insufficiency of the exocrine pancreas: vitamin B12 cannot be released from haptocorrin-vitamin B12 complexes.
   - Ileal resection, ileitis or diffuse ileal disease, including diffuse intestinal disease such as lymphoma and systemic sclerosis.
   - Competitive parasitic uptake: fish tapeworm, acquired by eating raw fish.
   - Bacterial overgrowth in blind loops and diverticula of the bowel.
3. **Increased requirement:** pregnancy, hyperthyroidism, disseminated cancer and chronic infection produce a relative deficiency even with normal absorption.
4. Recreational use of **nitrous oxide** oxidises and inactivates vitamin B12.

## Cardinal manifestations of vitamin B12 deficiency.

1. **Megaloblastic anaemia:** moderate to severe, insidious, with leucopenia and hypersegmented granulocytes; the anaemia is often quite severe when the patient presents.
2. **Neurological lesions:** found in about three-quarters of cases of florid pernicious anaemia, and can occur without overt haematological findings.
   - Demyelination of the dorsal and lateral spinal tracts, sometimes followed by loss of axons.
   - Spastic paraparesis, sensory ataxia and severe paraesthesias in the lower limbs.
   - Less frequently, degenerative changes in the ganglia of the posterior roots and in peripheral nerves.
3. **Gastrointestinal changes in pernicious anaemia:** atrophic glossitis (a shiny, glazed, "beefy" tongue) and chronic atrophic gastritis, with an increased risk of gastric carcinoma.
4. Raised plasma and urine **methylmalonic acid** and raised serum homocysteine.

## What is pernicious anemia?

**Pernicious anaemia** is a specific form of megaloblastic anaemia caused by an autoimmune gastritis that impairs the production of intrinsic factor, which is required for vitamin B12 uptake from the gut.

## Main pathogenesis of pernicious anaemia.

- Pernicious anaemia is believed to result from an **autoimmune attack on the gastric mucosa**, producing chronic atrophic gastritis with loss of parietal cells, a prominent infiltrate of lymphocytes and plasma cells, and megaloblastic changes in mucosal cells.
- Three types of autoantibody are present in many, but not all, patients:
  - **Type I** (about 75%): blocks the binding of vitamin B12 to intrinsic factor.
  - **Type II:** blocks binding of the intrinsic factor-vitamin B12 complex to cubilin, preventing intestinal absorption.
  - **Type III** (85% to 90%): recognises the alpha and beta subunits of the gastric proton pump; it is not specific, being found in as many as 50% of older adults with idiopathic chronic gastritis.
- Type I and type II antibodies are found in plasma and gastric juice.
- Autoantibodies are of diagnostic use but are not thought to be the primary cause of the gastric pathology; an **autoreactive T-cell response** initiates the gastric mucosal injury and triggers the formation of autoantibodies.
- When the mass of intrinsic factor-secreting cells falls below a threshold and stored vitamin B12 is depleted, anaemia develops.
- It is associated with other autoimmune disorders, particularly autoimmune thyroiditis and adrenalitis.

## How can you diagnose a case of pernicious anaemia?

The diagnosis is based on:

1. A moderate to severe **megaloblastic anaemia**.
2. **Leucopenia with hypersegmented granulocytes.**
3. Low serum vitamin B12.
4. Raised serum levels of homocysteine and methylmalonic acid.
5. **Serum antibodies to intrinsic factor**, which are highly specific for pernicious anaemia.

The diagnosis is confirmed by an outpouring of reticulocytes and a rise in haematocrit beginning about 5 days after parenteral administration of vitamin B12.

## Mention the causes of macrocytosis/macrocytic anemia.

**Megaloblastic**

1. **Vitamin B12 deficiency:** pernicious anaemia; gastrectomy (loss of intrinsic factor); achlorhydria; exocrine pancreatic insufficiency; ileal resection or disease; fish tapeworm; bacterial overgrowth in blind loops; increased demand (pregnancy, hyperthyroidism, disseminated cancer, chronic infection).
2. **Folate deficiency:**
   - Decreased intake: poor diet (alcohol use disorder, the indigent, the very old); malabsorption (sprue, lymphoma); drugs such as phenytoin and oral contraceptives.
   - Increased requirement: pregnancy, infancy, chronic haemolytic anaemia, disseminated cancer.
   - Impaired utilisation: folic acid antagonists such as methotrexate.
3. **Drugs inhibiting DNA synthesis:** many chemotherapeutic drugs.

**Nonmegaloblastic**

- Anaemia of hepatocellular disease, from lipid abnormalities that add phospholipid and cholesterol to red cell membranes.
- Aplastic anaemia, in which the red cells are usually slightly macrocytic.


## Write the laboratory investigation/blood picture of megaloblastic anemia.

**Blood**

- Macrocytic, oval red cells (macro-ovalocytes), highly characteristic; mean cell haemoglobin concentration not raised.
- Marked anisocytosis and poikilocytosis.
- Low reticulocyte count.
- Hypersegmented neutrophils, with five or more nuclear lobules.
- Nucleated red cell progenitors occasionally, in severe anaemia.
- Leucopenia and pancytopenia from ineffective haemopoiesis.

**Bone marrow**

- Markedly hypercellular.
- Megaloblastic change at all stages of erythroid development; large promegaloblasts with deeply basophilic cytoplasm and fine nuclear chromatin; nuclear to cytoplasmic asynchrony.
- Giant metamyelocytes and band forms; large megakaryocytes with bizarre multilobate nuclei.

**Biochemical**

- Vitamin B12 deficiency: low serum vitamin B12; raised serum homocysteine and methylmalonic acid; antibodies to intrinsic factor in pernicious anaemia.
- Folate deficiency: decreased serum or red cell folate; raised homocysteine; normal methylmalonate.
- Reticulocyte outpouring and rise in haematocrit about 5 days after vitamin B12.


## What is megaloblast?

**Megaloblasts** are the enlarged, abnormal haemopoietic precursors of megaloblastic anaemia. The most primitive (promegaloblasts) are large, with deeply basophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli and a fine nuclear chromatin; as they differentiate, the nucleus keeps its fine chromatin while haemoglobin accumulates normally in the cytoplasm, producing nuclear to cytoplasmic asynchrony.


## Write a short note on Schilling test.

The Schilling test [vitamin B12 absorption test] finds the cause of vitamin B12 deficiency by measuring absorption of an oral dose of radiolabelled vitamin B12 as excretion in the urine.

**Stage 1:** radiolabelled (cobalt) vitamin B12 is given by mouth, after an intramuscular dose of unlabelled vitamin B12 to saturate body stores; urine is collected for 24 hours. Less than 10% excreted indicates malabsorption.

**Stage 2:** the test is repeated with oral intrinsic factor.

- Normal excretion: lack of intrinsic factor, that is pernicious anaemia.
- Persistently low excretion: malabsorption from another cause, for example ileal disease.

Many laboratories no longer perform it, because the radiolabelled test substances are no longer produced.


## A patient after partial gastrectomy has pallor, weakness, neuropathy/paresthesia. What is your provisional diagnosis?

**Provisional diagnosis:** megaloblastic anaemia from vitamin B12 deficiency.

- Pallor and weakness: anaemia.
- Neuropathy and paraesthesia: demyelination of the dorsal and lateral spinal tracts, producing severe paraesthesiae in the lower limbs, sensory ataxia and spastic paraparesis; also peripheral nerve change.


## Same: what is the cause?

With gastrectomy, **intrinsic factor is lost**, so vitamin B12 cannot be absorbed. Once stored vitamin B12 is depleted, anaemia develops.


## Same: what is the hematological profile / how will you confirm?

1. **Blood:** macro-ovalocytes, hypersegmented neutrophils, low reticulocyte count, leucopenia.
2. **Marrow:** hypercellular, with megaloblastic change.
3. **Serum vitamin B12:** low.
4. **Homocysteine and methylmalonic acid:** raised.
5. **Therapeutic response:** reticulocyte outpouring and rise in haematocrit about 5 days after parenteral vitamin B12.


## Define haemolytic anaemia.

**Haemolytic** anaemias are the result of extravascular or intravascular haemolysis. They share the following features:

- A shortened red cell life span below the normal 120 days.
- Elevated erythropoietin levels and a compensatory increase in erythropoiesis.
- Accumulation of haemoglobin degradation products created during the process of red cell haemolysis.

## What do you mean by premature destruction of RBC?

Premature destruction of red cells is the removal of red cells before the normal life span of 120 days. It occurs in two ways:

- **Extravascular haemolysis:** in the great majority of haemolytic anaemias the premature destruction occurs within phagocytes. It is usually caused by alterations that make red cells less deformable, so that they are sequestered and phagocytosed by macrophages in the splenic cords. If persistent, it leads to hyperplasia of phagocytes and splenomegaly.
- **Intravascular haemolysis:** less common; caused by mechanical injury, complement fixation, intracellular parasites (for example falciparum malaria) or exogenous toxic factors.

## Outline classification of haemolytic anaemia.

| Mechanism | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Inherited genetic defects** | |
| Red cell membrane disorders | Hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis |
| Hexose monophosphate shunt enzyme deficiencies | G6PD deficiency, glutathione synthetase deficiency |
| Glycolytic enzyme deficiencies | Pyruvate kinase deficiency, hexokinase deficiency |
| Deficient globin synthesis | Thalassaemia syndromes |
| Structurally abnormal globins (haemoglobinopathies) | Sickle cell disease, unstable haemoglobins |
| **Acquired genetic defect** | |
| Deficiency of phosphatidylinositol-linked glycoproteins | Paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria |
| **Antibody-mediated destruction** | Haemolytic disease of the newborn (Rh disease), transfusion reactions, drug-induced, autoimmune disorders |
| **Mechanical trauma** | |
| Microangiopathic haemolytic anaemias | Haemolytic uraemic syndrome, disseminated intravascular coagulation, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura |
| Cardiac traumatic haemolysis | Defective cardiac valves |
| Repetitive physical trauma | Bongo drumming, marathon running, karate chopping |
| **Infections of red cells** | Malaria, babesiosis |
| **Toxic or chemical injury** | Clostridial sepsis, snake venom, lead poisoning |
| **Membrane lipid abnormalities** | Abetalipoproteinaemia, severe hepatocellular liver disease |
| **Sequestration** | Hypersplenism |

## Common examples of congenital haemolytic anaemias.

1. **Red cell membrane disorders:** hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis.
2. **Red cell enzyme deficiencies:** G6PD deficiency and glutathione synthetase deficiency (hexose monophosphate shunt); pyruvate kinase deficiency and hexokinase deficiency (glycolytic enzymes).
3. **Haemoglobin abnormalities:** thalassaemia syndromes (deficient globin synthesis); sickle cell disease and unstable haemoglobins (structurally abnormal globins).

## Haemolytic anaemias due to extracorpuscular defect.

Causes that act on the red cell from outside:

1. **Antibody-mediated destruction (immunohaemolytic anaemia):** warm antibody type (IgG), cold agglutinin type (IgM) and cold haemolysin type (IgG); also haemolytic disease of the newborn (Rh disease), transfusion reactions and drug-induced haemolysis.
2. **Mechanical trauma:** microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia (disseminated intravascular coagulation, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura, haemolytic uraemic syndrome), cardiac valve prostheses, and repetitive physical trauma.
3. **Infections of red cells:** malaria, babesiosis.
4. **Toxic or chemical injury:** clostridial sepsis, snake venom, lead poisoning.
5. **Membrane lipid abnormalities:** abetalipoproteinaemia, severe hepatocellular liver disease.
6. **Sequestration:** hypersplenism.

## Why does jaundice occur in haemolytic anaemia?

- Haem groups derived from haemoglobin are metabolised to **bilirubin** within mononuclear phagocytes, leading to jaundice.
- In all types of uncomplicated haemolytic anaemia the excess serum bilirubin is **unconjugated**.
- The level of hyperbilirubinaemia depends on the functional capacity of the liver and the rate of haemolysis. When the liver is normal, jaundice is rarely severe.
- Excessive bilirubin excreted by the liver into the biliary tract often leads to the formation of **pigment gallstones** derived from haem.

## Evidence of increased haemoglobin breakdown.

1. **Extravascular haemolysis**
   - Jaundice from unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia.
   - Variable decrease in plasma **haptoglobin**, because some haemoglobin escapes from phagocytes.
   - Haemosiderosis: accumulation of haemosiderin in the spleen, liver and bone marrow.
   - Pigment gallstones (cholelithiasis) with chronic haemolysis.
2. **Intravascular haemolysis**
   - Haemoglobinaemia and **haemoglobinuria**.
   - Haemosiderinuria; iron released from haemoglobin accumulates within renal tubular cells, giving renal haemosiderosis.
   - Free haemoglobin is bound by haptoglobin; as serum haptoglobin is depleted, free haemoglobin oxidises to brown methaemoglobin, which gives the urine a red-brown colour.
   - Jaundice, as bilirubin is formed in mononuclear phagocytes from the haemoglobin-haptoglobin complexes.

## Evidence of compensatory erythroid hyperplasia.

- Anaemia and lowered tissue oxygen tension trigger the production of **erythropoietin**, which stimulates erythroid differentiation.
- Bone marrow shows increased numbers of **erythroid precursors** (normoblasts).
- Peripheral blood shows a prominent **reticulocytosis**.
- If the anaemia is severe, **extramedullary haematopoiesis** appears in the liver, spleen and lymph nodes.
- In sickle cell disease and thalassaemia major, marked marrow expansion leads to bone resorption and secondary new bone formation, giving prominent cheekbones and a "crewcut" appearance of the skull on radiographs.

## Evidence of haemolysis in PBF.

1. **Reticulocytosis**, with larger red cells that show blue-red polychromatophilic cytoplasm.
2. Anisocytosis and poikilocytosis, with changes specific to the cause:
   - **Spherocytes:** small, dark-staining red cells lacking central pallor, in hereditary spherocytosis and autoimmune haemolytic anaemia.
   - **Heinz bodies [denatured haemoglobin inclusions in red cells] and bite cells**, in G6PD deficiency.
   - **Irreversibly sickled cells** and target cells, in sickle cell disease.
   - **Target cells**, basophilic stippling and fragmented red cells, in thalassaemia.
   - **Schistocytes (fragmented red cells)**, burr cells, helmet cells and triangle cells, in microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia and with defective cardiac valves.
3. **Howell-Jolly bodies** [nuclear remnants in red cells, seen after splenectomy] (small nuclear remnants) when the spleen is absent or nonfunctional.

## Conditions where nucleated RBCs are seen in PBF.

1. **Severe megaloblastic anaemia:** nucleated red cell progenitors occasionally appear in the circulating blood when the anaemia is severe.
2. **Thalassaemia major:** poorly haemoglobinised normoblasts, from "stress" erythropoiesis and abnormal release of red cell precursors from sites of extramedullary haematopoiesis.
3. **Myelophthisic anaemia:** marrow replaced by metastatic cancer, granulomatous disease or the fibrosis of the spent phase of myeloproliferative neoplasms; nucleated erythroid precursors and immature granulocytic forms are released prematurely (**leukoerythroblastosis**), with teardrop-shaped red cells.

## What is autoimmune haemolytic anaemia (AIHA)?

Immunohaemolytic anaemia is caused by antibodies that recognise red cells and cause their premature destruction. Although these disorders are commonly referred to as **autoimmune haemolytic** anaemias, the designation immunohaemolytic anaemia is preferred because the immune reaction is initiated in some instances by an ingested drug.

## Causes of AIHA.

| Type | Antibody | Causes |
|---|---|---|
| **Warm antibody type** (about 80% of cases) | IgG (less commonly IgA), active at 37°C | About 50% idiopathic (primary). Secondary: autoimmune disorders (particularly systemic lupus erythematosus), drugs, lymphoid neoplasms |
| **Cold agglutinin type** (15% to 20% of cases) | IgM, active below 37°C (binds at 0°C to 4°C) | Acute: mycoplasmal infection, infectious mononucleosis (also cytomegalovirus, influenza and HIV). Chronic: B-cell neoplasms or idiopathic |
| **Cold haemolysin type** | IgG, active below 37°C (binds the P blood group antigen) | Rare; mainly in children following viral infections |

Drug-induced warm antibody type acts by two mechanisms:

- **Antigenic drugs**, for example penicillin and cephalosporins: the drug binds to the red cell membrane and creates a new antigenic determinant; haemolysis follows large intravenous doses, 1 to 2 weeks after therapy starts.
- **Tolerance-breaking drugs**, of which **alpha-methyldopa** is the prototype: they lead to antibodies against red cell antigens, particularly Rh antigens; about 10% of patients develop autoantibodies and roughly 1% develop clinically significant haemolysis.

## Classify haemolytic anemia.

| Mechanism | Examples |
|---|---|
| Inherited genetic defects: red cell membrane disorders | Hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis |
| Inherited genetic defects: enzyme deficiencies | Hexose monophosphate shunt enzyme deficiencies (G6PD deficiency, glutathione synthetase deficiency); glycolytic enzyme deficiencies (pyruvate kinase deficiency, hexokinase deficiency) |
| Inherited genetic defects: haemoglobin abnormalities | Deficient globin synthesis (thalassaemia syndromes); structurally abnormal globins (sickle cell disease, unstable haemoglobins) |
| **Acquired genetic defects** | Deficiency of phosphatidylinositol-linked glycoproteins (paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria) |
| **Antibody-mediated destruction** | Haemolytic disease of the newborn (Rh disease), transfusion reactions, drug-induced, autoimmune disorders |
| **Mechanical trauma** | Microangiopathic haemolytic anaemias (haemolytic uraemic syndrome, disseminated intravascular coagulation, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura); cardiac traumatic haemolysis (defective cardiac valves); repetitive physical trauma (bongo drumming, marathon running, karate chopping) |
| **Infections of red cells** | Malaria, babesiosis |
| **Toxic or chemical injury** | Clostridial sepsis, snake venom, lead poisoning |
| **Membrane lipid abnormalities** | Abetalipoproteinaemia, severe hepatocellular liver disease |
| **Sequestration** | Hypersplenism |

## Compare and contrast warm antibody and cold antibody.

| Feature | Warm antibody type | Cold agglutinin type |
|---|---|---|
| Antibody | **IgG** (less commonly IgA), active at 37°C | **IgM**, binds avidly at 0°C to 4°C but not at 37°C |
| Frequency | About 80% of immunohaemolytic anaemia | 15% to 20% |
| Cause | About 50% idiopathic (primary); others secondary to autoimmune disorders (particularly systemic lupus erythematosus), drugs or lymphoid neoplasms | Transient after infections (Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Epstein-Barr virus [herpesvirus that causes infectious mononucleosis], cytomegalovirus, influenza, HIV); chronic form with B-cell neoplasms or idiopathic |
| Mechanism | IgG-coated red cells bind Fc receptors on phagocytes, which remove red cell membrane (partial phagocytosis); cells become **spherocytes** | IgM binds in cold parts of the body, agglutinates red cells and fixes complement; as blood warms IgM is released, leaving sublytic C3b, which acts as an opsonin |
| Haemolysis | Mostly extravascular, in the spleen; moderate splenomegaly | Extravascular in spleen, liver and bone marrow; intravascular haemolysis usually not seen |
| Clinical features | Anaemia, splenomegaly, jaundice | Pallor, cyanosis and Raynaud phenomenon [episodic digital vasospasm] in fingers, toes and ears exposed to cold |
| Treatment | Remove initiating factors such as drugs; immunosuppressive drugs and splenectomy | Avoid cold temperatures |

Cold haemolysin type (paroxysmal cold haemoglobinuria): IgG antibodies against the P blood group antigen bind in cool peripheral regions, and complement-mediated lysis occurs in the warm core of the body; mostly in children after viral infections.

## Narrate the features of haemolysis found in PBF.

- **Reticulocytosis**, which is prominent, from the compensatory increase in erythropoiesis.
- **Spherocytes** in warm antibody immunohaemolytic anaemia, as in hereditary spherocytosis, from loss of red cell membrane.
- **Red cell fragments (schistocytes), burr cells, helmet cells and triangle cells** in haemolysis due to red cell trauma, such as cardiac valve prostheses and microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia.

## Write down the general evidence/laboratory features of haemolysis.

1. Shortened red cell life span, below the normal 120 days.
2. Anaemia, with raised erythropoietin levels.
3. **Reticulocytosis** in the peripheral blood.
4. Increased erythroid precursors (normoblasts) in the bone marrow.
5. Hyperbilirubinaemia, in which the excess serum bilirubin is **unconjugated**; jaundice follows.
6. Raised lactate dehydrogenase and low plasma **haptoglobin**.
7. **Haemosiderosis**, with haemosiderin in the spleen, liver and bone marrow.
8. Extramedullary haematopoiesis in the liver, spleen and lymph nodes if the anaemia is severe.
9. Pigment gallstones in chronic haemolysis.
10. Extravascular haemolysis: splenomegaly, from hyperplasia of phagocytes.
11. Intravascular haemolysis: haemoglobinaemia, haemoglobinuria and haemosiderinuria; no splenomegaly.
12. Positive direct Coombs test [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells] in immunohaemolytic anaemia.

## Write down the evidence of intravascular haemolysis.

1. Anaemia.
2. **Haemoglobinaemia**: free haemoglobin from lysed red cells in the plasma.
3. **Haemoglobinuria**: some filtered haemoglobin and methaemoglobin passes out in the urine, giving a red-brown colour.
4. **Haemosiderinuria** and renal haemosiderosis: iron released from haemoglobin accumulates in renal tubular cells.
5. **Jaundice**: haem from haemoglobin-haptoglobin complexes is metabolised to bilirubin in mononuclear phagocytes.
6. Serum haptoglobin is depleted; free haemoglobin then oxidises to **methaemoglobin**, which is brown.
7. Splenomegaly is not seen.
8. In chronic haemolysis, iron loss in the urine may lead to iron deficiency.

Causes: mechanical injury, complement fixation, intracellular parasites (falciparum malaria) and toxic factors such as clostridial sepsis.

## Write a short note on Coombs' test (direct).

**Principle.** The direct Coombs [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells] antiglobulin test detects antibodies or complement on the surface of the patient's red cells. The patient's red cells are mixed with sera containing antibodies specific for human immunoglobulin or complement. If either is present on the red cells, the antibodies cause **agglutination**, seen as clumping.

**Uses**
- Diagnosis of **immunohaemolytic anaemia**, which requires detection of antibodies or complement on the patient's red cells.
- Acute haemolytic transfusion reaction: typically positive, unless all the donor red cells have lysed.
- Delayed haemolytic transfusion reaction: positive.
- About 10% of patients taking alpha-methyldopa develop autoantibodies detected by the direct Coombs test; roughly 1% develop clinically significant haemolysis.

**Indirect Coombs test.** The patient's serum is tested for its ability to agglutinate commercially available red cells bearing known antigens. It characterises the target antigen and the temperature dependence of the antibody.

## What do you mean by haemolytic disease of newborn?

Immune hydrops is a **haemolytic disease** caused by blood group antigen incompatibility between mother and foetus.

## Normal types of haemoglobin.

Haemoglobin is a tetrameric protein composed of two pairs of globin chains, each with its own haem group.

| Type | Chains | Occurrence |
|---|---|---|
| **HbA** | Two alpha and two beta | The main haemoglobin of normal adult red cells |
| **HbA2** | Two alpha and two delta | Small amounts; normal level 2.5% plus or minus 0.3% of total haemoglobin |
| **HbF** (foetal haemoglobin) | Two alpha and two gamma | Small amounts in adults; the level normally falls in the first months after birth |

## Name / classify congenital haemolytic anaemias due to defect in haemoglobin.

1. **Deficient globin synthesis:** thalassaemia syndromes, which are alpha-thalassaemia and beta-thalassaemia.
2. **Structurally abnormal globins (haemoglobinopathies):** sickle cell disease and unstable haemoglobins.

## Define haemoglobinopathies.

**Haemoglobinopathies** are disorders caused by mutations in globin genes that produce structurally abnormal globins. Several hundred are known.

## Examples of haemoglobinopathies.

1. **Sickle cell disease:** a missense mutation in the beta-globin gene gives sickle haemoglobin (HbS).
2. **HbSC disease:** the compound heterozygous state for HbS and HbC.
3. **HbC:** a variant haemoglobin common in regions where HbS is found.
4. **Unstable haemoglobins.**

## Name the normal (and abnormal) haemoglobins.

| Type | Haemoglobin | Chain composition |
|---|---|---|
| **Normal** | HbA (mainly, in adult red cells) | α2β2 |
| | HbA2 (small amounts; normally 2.5% plus or minus 0.3%) | α2δ2 |
| | HbF (foetal haemoglobin) | α2γ2 |
| **Abnormal** | HbS (sickle haemoglobin; valine replaces glutamate in β-globin) | α2βS2 |
| | HbC | Variant haemoglobin common where HbS is found |
| | HbH | Tetramer of β-globin chains (β4), in α-thalassaemia |
| | Haemoglobin Barts | Tetramer of γ-globin chains (γ4), in α-thalassaemia |
| | Unstable haemoglobins | Structurally abnormal globins |


## Write a short note on hemoglobinopathy.

**Definition:** inherited disorders caused by mutations in globin genes that produce structurally abnormal haemoglobin; several hundred are known. **Sickle cell disease** is the commonest and the one considered here.

**Pathogenesis of sickle cell disease**

1. A missense mutation in the β-globin gene replaces glutamate with valine, producing HbS.
2. Deoxygenated HbS stacks into polymers, turning the red cell cytosol into a viscous gel and then into needle-like fibres, which distort the cell into a sickle or holly-leaf shape.
3. Repeated sickling damages the membrane; cells become dehydrated, dense and rigid, and finally irreversibly sickled; these are removed by mononuclear phagocytes (extravascular haemolysis), and fragile cells cause some intravascular haemolysis.
4. Sickled cells are sticky, with increased adhesion molecules; microvascular occlusion is helped by inflammation and vasoconstriction, which slow the passage of red cells.
5. Sickling is affected by HbS with other haemoglobins (HbA and HbF inhibit polymerisation), mean cell haemoglobin concentration, intracellular pH and transit time through microvascular beds.

**Morphology**

- Blood: irreversibly sickled cells, reticulocytosis, target cells; Howell-Jolly bodies [nuclear remnants in red cells, seen after splenectomy] from asplenia.
- Hyperplastic marrow, with bone resorption and a "crewcut" skull on X-ray; extramedullary haemopoiesis.
- Hyperbilirubinaemia and pigment gallstones.
- Spleen: autoinfarcted remnant.

**Clinical features**

- Chronic haemolytic anaemia, microvascular occlusions and ischaemic tissue damage.
- Chronic hypoxia: impaired growth and development; damage to spleen, heart, kidneys and lungs; hyposthenuria.
- Increased susceptibility to infection with encapsulated organisms (Pneumococcus, Haemophilus influenzae, meningococcus), because of impaired splenic function.
- Infants become symptomatic at 5 or 6 months, when HbF falls.
- Diagnosis: irreversibly sickled cells and tests for sickle haemoglobin; treatment with hydroxyurea, which raises HbF.


## Define thalassemia.

**Thalassaemia** is a genetically heterogeneous disorder caused by germline mutations that decrease the synthesis of either alpha-globin or beta-globin, leading to anaemia, tissue hypoxia, and red cell haemolysis related to the imbalance in globin chain synthesis.

## How is thalassemia classified?

Thalassaemia is classified by the globin chain that is deficient, and then by clinical severity.

| Syndrome | Genetic basis | Clinical features |
|---|---|---|
| **Beta-thalassaemia major** | Two beta-thalassaemia alleles (beta-zero or beta-plus) | Severe; requires blood transfusions |
| **Beta-thalassaemia intermedia** | Milder variants of two defective alleles, and unusual heterozygous forms | Severe, but does not require regular blood transfusions |
| **Beta-thalassaemia minor** (trait) | One beta-thalassaemia allele and one normal | Asymptomatic, with mild or absent anaemia; red cell abnormalities seen |
| **Silent carrier** (alpha) | Deletion of one alpha-globin gene | Asymptomatic; no red cell abnormality |
| **Alpha-thalassaemia trait** | Deletion of two alpha-globin genes | Asymptomatic, like beta-thalassaemia minor |
| **Haemoglobin H disease** | Deletion of three alpha-globin genes | Severe; resembles beta-thalassaemia intermedia |
| **Alpha-thalassaemia major** (hydrops fetalis) | Deletion of all four alpha-globin genes | Lethal in utero without transfusions |

## Clinical features of thalassaemia.

**Beta-thalassaemia major**

- The anaemia manifests 6 to 9 months after birth, as haemoglobin synthesis switches from HbF to HbA; in untransfused patients haemoglobin is 3 to 6 g/dL.
- Untreated children have growth retardation and die early from the effects of anaemia; severe cachexia occurs in untreated patients.
- Hepatosplenomegaly from extramedullary haematopoiesis; the spleen may weigh as much as 1500 g.
- Skeletal changes: expanding marrow erodes cortical bone, giving a "crewcut" skull on radiographs; in survivors the cheekbones and other bony prominences are enlarged and distorted.
- **Iron overload** (haemosiderosis and secondary haemochromatosis) from increased gut absorption and repeated transfusions damages the heart, liver and pancreas; cardiac disease is an important cause of death.
- With transfusions and iron chelation, survival into the third decade is possible.

**Beta-thalassaemia minor**

- Usually asymptomatic; anaemia, if present, is mild.

**Alpha-thalassaemia**

- Silent carrier: asymptomatic, with slight microcytosis. Trait: small red cells, minimal or no anaemia, no abnormal physical signs.
- **Haemoglobin H disease:** moderately severe anaemia; HbH has very high oxygen affinity, so tissue hypoxia is out of proportion to the haemoglobin level.
- **Hydrops fetalis:** foetal distress by the third trimester, severe pallor, generalised oedema and massive hepatosplenomegaly; lifelong transfusion dependence in survivors.

## Type of mutation in beta thalassaemia.

Beta-thalassaemia is caused by more than 100 different mutations, mostly **point mutations**, of two kinds:

- **Beta-zero mutations:** absent beta-globin synthesis.
- **Beta-plus mutations:** reduced but detectable beta-globin synthesis.

They fall into three major classes:

1. **Splicing mutations:** the most common cause of beta-plus thalassaemia. Some destroy normal RNA splice junctions and prevent production of normal beta-globin mRNA (beta-zero); others create an ectopic splice site within an intron, so that some normal mRNA is still made (beta-plus).
2. **Promoter region mutations:** reduce transcription by 75% to 80%; associated with beta-plus thalassaemia.
3. **Chain terminator mutations:** the most common cause of beta-zero thalassaemia; nonsense mutations that introduce a premature stop codon, or small insertions or deletions that shift the reading frame (frameshift mutations).

## Laboratory diagnosis of thalassaemia.

1. **Peripheral blood:** anisocytosis, poikilocytosis, microcytosis and hypochromia; target cells, basophilic stippling and fragmented red cells; reticulocyte count elevated.
2. **Haemoglobin electrophoresis**
   - Beta-thalassaemia major: HbF markedly elevated; HbA completely absent (beta-zero/beta-zero) or present in small amounts.
   - Beta-thalassaemia minor: HbA2 increased to 4% to 8% of total haemoglobin (normal 2.5% plus or minus 0.3%); HbF normal or occasionally slightly increased.
   - Alpha-thalassaemia trait: HbA2 normal or low.
3. **Exclude iron deficiency**, the most common cause of microcytic anaemia, by serum iron, total iron-binding capacity and serum ferritin; the raised HbA2 is useful in females of childbearing age, who are at high risk of iron deficiency.
4. **Bone marrow:** mild erythroid hyperplasia in the minor form; striking expansion of haematopoietically active marrow in the major form.
5. **Prenatal diagnosis** is possible by molecular analysis of DNA.

## PBF findings of thalassaemia major.

- Marked variation in red cell size (anisocytosis) and shape (poikilocytosis).
- Microcytosis and hypochromia.
- **Target cells** (haemoglobin collects in the centre of the cell), basophilic stippling and fragmented red cells.
- Reticulocyte count elevated, but lower than expected for the severity of the anaemia because of ineffective erythropoiesis.
- Variable numbers of poorly haemoglobinised nucleated red cell precursors (normoblasts).
- Inclusions of aggregated alpha chains are efficiently removed by the spleen and are not easily seen.

## Confirmatory test for thalassaemia.

Diagnostic tests:

- **Haemoglobin electrophoresis:** in beta-thalassaemia major the major haemoglobin is HbF, markedly elevated, with HbA absent or small; in beta-thalassaemia minor HbA2 is increased to 4% to 8%.
- **Molecular analysis of DNA** for prenatal diagnosis.

## Write the genetic basis of β-thalassemia major.

- The β-globin chains are encoded by a single β-globin gene on **chromosome 11**.
- β-Thalassaemia major results from two β-thalassaemia alleles: β+/β+, β+/β0 or β0/β0.
- **β0 mutations:** absent β-globin synthesis. **β+ mutations:** reduced but detectable β-globin synthesis.
- More than 100 causative mutations are known, mostly point mutations, in three classes:
  1. **Splicing mutations:** the commonest cause of β+-thalassaemia; those destroying normal splice junctions cause β0-thalassaemia.
  2. **Promoter region mutations:** reduce transcription by 75% to 80%; β+-thalassaemia.
  3. **Chain terminator mutations:** the commonest cause of β0-thalassaemia; nonsense mutations with a premature stop codon, or frameshift insertions or deletions.
- Consequence: unpaired α chains precipitate in red cell precursors, causing membrane damage and apoptosis (70% to 85% of precursors in severe disease), ineffective erythropoiesis and haemolysis.


## Give the laboratory diagnosis of thalassemia/β-thalassemia major.

**Blood**

- Haemoglobin 3 to 6 g/dL in untransfused patients.
- Smear: anisocytosis, poikilocytosis, microcytosis and hypochromia; target cells, basophilic stippling and fragmented red cells; nucleated red cell precursors (normoblasts).
- Reticulocyte count raised, but lower than expected for the degree of anaemia, because of ineffective erythropoiesis.

**Haemoglobin analysis**

- HbA absent (β0/β0) or present in small amounts (β+/β+ or β0/β+).
- HbF markedly elevated and the major haemoglobin.
- In β-thalassaemia trait (minor): HbA2 increased to 4% to 8% (normal 2.5% plus or minus 0.3%); HbF normal or slightly increased.

**Other findings:** striking marrow expansion; "crewcut" appearance of the skull on X-ray; splenomegaly up to 1500 g; iron overload.


## A child has severe anemia, mongoloid face, hepatosplenomegaly, repeated transfusions, target cells. What is your probable diagnosis?

**Probable diagnosis:** β-thalassaemia major.

- Anaemia appears 6 to 9 months after birth, as haemoglobin synthesis switches from HbF to HbA.
- Marrow expansion in facial bones and skull alters the face.
- Hepatosplenomegaly from extramedullary haemopoiesis.
- Target cells on the blood smear.
- Repeated transfusions are required, with the risk of iron overload.


## Same child: how will you confirm the diagnosis?

1. **Blood smear:** microcytosis, hypochromia, anisocytosis, poikilocytosis, target cells, basophilic stippling, fragmented cells and normoblasts.
2. **Haemoglobin analysis:** HbA absent or reduced; HbF markedly elevated.
3. **Reticulocyte count:** raised, but lower than expected.
4. **X-ray of skull:** "crewcut" appearance.
5. **Iron studies:** iron overload.


## Which type of abnormal haemoglobin is found in sickle cell anaemia?

**Sickle haemoglobin (HbS).** A missense mutation in the beta-globin gene replaces a charged glutamate residue with a hydrophobic valine residue. In affected individuals almost all the haemoglobin in the red cell is HbS (two alpha chains and two sickle beta chains).

## Clinical features of sickle cell anaemia.

- Infants become symptomatic only at 5 or 6 months of age, when the level of HbF normally falls.
- **Chronic haemolytic anaemia** with jaundice and pigment gallstones; chronic tissue hypoxia impairs growth and development and damages the spleen, heart, kidneys and lungs.
- The course is punctuated by **crises**:
  - **Vaso-occlusive (pain) crises:** hypoxic injury and infarction causing severe pain, most often in the bones, lungs, liver, brain, spleen and penis; in children, bone crises and the hand-foot syndrome (dactylitis).
  - **Acute chest syndrome:** fever, cough, chest pain and pulmonary infiltrates; may be fatal.
  - **Sequestration crisis** in children with an intact spleen: massive entrapment of sickled cells, rapid splenic enlargement, hypovolaemia and sometimes shock.
  - **Aplastic crisis:** parvovirus B19 infection of red cell progenitors causes a sudden worsening of the anaemia.
- **Priapism** in up to 45% of males after puberty; **stroke** and **retinopathy** with loss of vision.
- **Autosplenectomy:** the spleen is enlarged in early childhood, then infarcted and fibrosed to a small nubbin by adolescence or early adulthood.
- Increased susceptibility to infection with encapsulated organisms (Pneumococcus pneumoniae, Haemophilus influenzae, meningococcus), from impaired splenic function and defects in the alternative complement pathway.
- **Hyposthenuria** (inability to concentrate urine) from sickling in the renal medulla.
- **Leg ulcers** in adults from vascular stagnation in subcutaneous tissues.
- Marrow expansion gives prominent cheekbones and a "crewcut" skull; cor pulmonale may follow pulmonary vessel infarcts.

## How does leg ulcer occur in sickle cell anaemia?

In adult patients, **vascular stagnation in subcutaneous tissues** often leads to leg ulcers; the complication is rare in children.

The stagnation reflects the microvascular occlusion that underlies the disease:

- Sickling occurs within microvascular beds with slow transit times.
- Sickle red cells express increased amounts of adhesion molecules and are sticky, and inflammation slows or arrests their movement through the microvasculature.
- Stagnation prolongs exposure to low oxygen tension, so more cells sickle and obstruct the vessel: a vicious cycle of sickling, obstruction, hypoxia and more sickling.
- Free haemoglobin from lysed sickle cells binds and inactivates nitric oxide, which raises vascular tone and enhances platelet aggregation.

## Laboratory diagnosis of sickle cell anaemia.

1. **Peripheral blood:** variable numbers of **irreversibly sickled cells**, reticulocytosis and target cells (from red cell dehydration); Howell-Jolly bodies [nuclear remnants in red cells, seen after splenectomy] from asplenia; anisocytosis and poikilocytosis.
2. **Bone marrow:** hyperplastic from compensatory erythroid hyperplasia.
3. **Hyperbilirubinaemia** from the increased breakdown of haemoglobin.
4. **Confirmation:** the diagnosis is suggested by the clinical findings and the presence of irreversibly sickled red cells, and is confirmed by various tests for sickle haemoglobin.
5. **Prenatal diagnosis** by analysis of foetal DNA obtained by amniocentesis or chorionic biopsy.
6. **Newborn screening** for sickle haemoglobin, using heel-stick samples obtained at birth.

## What is pancytopenia?

**Pancytopenia** is the combination of anaemia, neutropenia and thrombocytopenia.

## Write down the important causes of pancytopenia.

1. **Aplastic anaemia:** chronic primary haematopoietic failure, from:
   - Chemicals and drugs: dose-related marrow suppression (many cancer chemotherapy drugs, benzene) or idiosyncratic reactions (chloramphenicol, gold salts).
   - Viral infections, most commonly viral hepatitis (about 5% of cases).
   - Whole-body irradiation.
   - Inherited defects: Fanconi anaemia [autosomal recessive defect in DNA repair] and telomerase defects.
   - Idiopathic in about 65% of cases.
2. **Megaloblastic anaemia:** impaired DNA synthesis causes most precursors to undergo apoptosis in the marrow (ineffective haematopoiesis).
3. **Bone marrow replacement:** leukaemia, disseminated cancer and granulomatous disease (myelophthisic marrow failure).
4. **Ineffective haematopoiesis:** myelodysplastic neoplasm.
5. **Acute myeloid leukaemia:** replacement of the marrow with blasts.
6. **Hypersplenism:** sequestration of blood cells in an enlarged spleen.
7. **Hairy cell leukaemia:** pancytopenia from marrow involvement and sequestration of cells in the enlarged spleen, in more than one-half of cases.

## How will you diagnose/investigate a case of pancytopenia?

1. **Clinical examination for splenomegaly:** characteristically absent in aplastic anaemia; if present, the diagnosis of aplastic anaemia should be questioned.
2. **Blood count and film:** in aplastic anaemia the red cells are usually slightly macrocytic and normochromic, with reticulocytopenia; in megaloblastic anaemia look for macro-ovalocytes and neutrophils with five or more lobes.
3. **Bone marrow biopsy:** the diagnosis of aplastic anaemia rests on it; aspirates often give little material (a "dry tap"), so aplasia is best seen in the biopsy.
   - Aplastic anaemia: markedly hypocellular marrow.
   - "Aleukaemic" leukaemia and myelodysplastic neoplasm: usually a hypercellular marrow filled with neoplastic progenitors.
   - Megaloblastic anaemia: markedly hypercellular marrow with megaloblasts.
4. **Look for the cause:** serum vitamin B12 and folate levels if the picture is megaloblastic; history of drug, chemical, viral or radiation exposure for aplastic anaemia.

## Blood and bone marrow picture of aplastic anaemia.

**Blood**

- Pancytopenia: anaemia, neutropenia and thrombocytopenia.
- Red cells usually slightly macrocytic and normochromic.
- Reticulocytopenia is the rule.

**Bone marrow**

- Markedly hypocellular, largely devoid of haematopoietic cells.
- Often only fat cells, fibrous stroma, and scattered lymphocytes and plasma cells remain.
- Aspirates often yield little material (a "dry tap"); aplasia is best appreciated in marrow biopsies.
- If multiple transfusions are needed, systemic haemosiderosis can appear.

## Why does pancytopenia occur in acute leukaemia?

- In **acute myeloid leukaemia**, the replacement of the marrow with blasts produces **marrow failure**, with anaemia, thrombocytopenia and neutropenia.
- In **acute lymphoblastic leukaemia** with a leukaemic presentation, the marrow is hypercellular and packed with lymphoblasts, which replace normal marrow elements.

## Why does pancytopenia occur in some cases of splenomegaly?

- Splenomegaly can cause **hypersplenism**, characterised by anaemia, leucopenia and/or thrombocytopenia.
- The probable cause of the cytopenias is **increased sequestration of formed elements** and the consequent enhanced phagocytosis by the splenic macrophages.
- The spleen normally sequesters 30% to 35% of the body's platelets, which can rise to 80% to 90% when the spleen is enlarged.
- In congestive splenomegaly, slowing of blood flow from the cords to the sinusoids prolongs the exposure of blood cells to macrophages, resulting in excessive destruction.
- In hairy cell leukaemia, pancytopenia results from marrow involvement and sequestration of cells in the enlarged spleen.

## How will you differentiate aplastic anaemia from subleukaemic acute leukaemia in the laboratory?

**Aplastic anaemia** must be distinguished from "aleukaemic" leukaemia and myelodysplastic neoplasm, which may have identical clinical manifestations. The diagnosis rests on examination of a bone marrow biopsy.

| Feature | Aplastic anaemia | Aleukaemic acute leukaemia |
|---|---|---|
| **Bone marrow cellularity** | Hypocellular, usually markedly so | Hypercellular |
| **Bone marrow cells** | Largely fat cells, fibrous stroma, scattered lymphocytes and plasma cells | Packed with neoplastic blasts, which replace normal marrow elements |
| **Marrow aspirate** | Often a "dry tap" | Not stated |
| **Spleen** | Splenomegaly characteristically absent | Not stated |

## Write down the causes of marrow aplasia.

1. **Chemicals and drugs** (most cases of known aetiology)
   - Dose-related and reversible marrow suppression: many cancer chemotherapy drugs and the organic solvent benzene.
   - Idiosyncratic and unpredictable: chloramphenicol and gold salts.
2. **Viral infections:** viral hepatitis (about 5% of cases), cytomegalovirus, Epstein-Barr virus [herpesvirus that causes infectious mononucleosis] and herpes zoster (varicella zoster).
3. **Whole-body irradiation:** therapeutic irradiation or nuclear accidents, which destroy haemopoietic stem cells in a dose-dependent fashion.
4. **Fanconi anaemia [inherited DNA-repair defect causing marrow failure]:** autosomal recessive defect of a multiprotein complex required for DNA repair, with congenital anomalies such as kidney and spleen hypoplasia and thumb or radial anomalies.
5. **Telomerase defects:** found in 5% to 10% of adult-onset aplastic anaemia; abnormally short telomeres are found in the marrow cells of as many as half of those affected.
6. **Idiopathic:** about 65% of cases.


## What are the clinical presentations of pancytopenia?

Pancytopenia, as seen in aplastic anaemia, presents according to the cell line affected:

1. **Anaemia:** progressive weakness, pallor and dyspnoea.
2. **Thrombocytopenia:** petechiae and ecchymoses.
3. **Neutropenia:** frequent and persistent infections, or sudden chills, fever and prostration.

Splenomegaly is characteristically absent; if present, the diagnosis of aplastic anaemia should be questioned.


## Give the laboratory diagnosis of aplastic anemia (PBF and bone marrow findings).

**Peripheral blood**

- Pancytopenia.
- Red cells usually slightly macrocytic and normochromic.
- Reticulocytopenia.

**Bone marrow**

- Markedly hypocellular, largely devoid of haemopoietic cells; only fat cells, fibrous stroma and scattered lymphocytes and plasma cells remain.
- Aspirates often yield little material (dry tap), so aplasia is best appreciated in marrow biopsies.

The diagnosis rests on bone marrow biopsy. It distinguishes aplastic anaemia from aleukaemic leukaemia and myelodysplastic neoplasm, which have hypercellular marrows filled with neoplastic progenitors.


## A boy of 11y has Hb 2 g, WBC 500/cmm, platelets 12000/cmm. What is the probable diagnosis?

**Probable diagnosis:** aplastic anaemia, with pancytopenia affecting all three cell lines.

**Confirmation:** bone marrow biopsy showing a markedly hypocellular marrow; other causes of pancytopenia, such as aleukaemic leukaemia and myelodysplastic neoplasm, have hypercellular marrows.


## Define hemorrhagic disorders with example.

Haemorrhagic diatheses (bleeding **disorders**) are conditions in which excessive bleeding results from (1) increased fragility of vessels, (2) platelet deficiency or dysfunction, and (3) derangement of coagulation, alone or in combination.

Examples:

- **Vessel wall**: hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia.
- **Platelets**: immune thrombocytopenic purpura.
- **Coagulation**: haemophilia A.

## Classify haemorrhagic disorder.

| Group | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Vessel wall abnormalities** | Infections (meningococcaemia, septicaemia, infective endocarditis, rickettsioses); drug reactions; scurvy and Ehlers-Danlos syndrome [inherited collagen disorder with hyperextensible skin and joints]; Cushing syndrome [glucocorticoid excess] and ageing; Henoch-Schönlein purpura [IgA vasculitis]; hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia; perivascular amyloidosis |
| **Platelet disorders** | Thrombocytopenia (decreased production, decreased survival, sequestration, dilution); immune thrombocytopenic purpura; thrombotic microangiopathies (TTP and HUS) |
| **Coagulation disorders** | Inherited: haemophilia A, haemophilia B, von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor]. Acquired: vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, disseminated intravascular coagulation |

## Approach to diagnosing a patient with a bleeding disorder.

Excessive bleeding results from vessel fragility, platelet deficiency or dysfunction, or derangement of coagulation. The clinical pattern is matched with laboratory tests.

| Pattern | Clinical picture | Laboratory findings |
|---|---|---|
| **Vessel wall** | Small haemorrhages (petechiae and purpura) in skin and mucous membranes, particularly the gingivae; occasionally joints, muscles, menorrhagia, nosebleeds, gastrointestinal bleeding, haematuria | Platelet count, PT and PTT usually normal, pointing by exclusion to the vessel wall |
| **Thrombocytopenia** | Bleeding from small vessels into skin and the mucous membranes of the gastrointestinal and genitourinary tracts; intracranial bleeding is most feared | Platelet count below 150,000 per µL; PT and PTT normal when thrombocytopenia is isolated |
| **Coagulation factor deficiency** | Large post-traumatic ecchymoses or haematomas; prolonged bleeding from a laceration or after surgery; bleeding into the gastrointestinal and urinary tracts and weight-bearing joints (haemarthrosis) | Prolonged PT and/or PTT; specific factor assays |

Tests used:

- **Prothrombin time (PT):** assesses the extrinsic and common pathways; prolonged by deficiency or dysfunction of factor V, factor VII, factor X, prothrombin or fibrinogen.
- **Partial thromboplastin time (PTT):** assesses the intrinsic and common pathways; prolonged by deficiency or dysfunction of factors V, VIII, IX, X, XI or XII, prothrombin or fibrinogen, or by interfering antiphospholipid antibodies.
- **Platelet count:** reference range 150,000 to 350,000 per µL; confirm abnormal counts on a peripheral blood film, as clumping can cause spurious thrombocytopenia.
- **Tests of platelet function:** platelet aggregation, and quantitative and qualitative tests of von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII].
- **Specific tests:** levels of specific clotting factors, fibrinogen, fibrin split products and circulating anticoagulants.

## What are the causes of haemorrhagic disorders?

1. **Vessel wall abnormalities**
   - Infections (meningococcaemia, other septicaemias, infective endocarditis, rickettsioses): microbial damage to the microvasculature (vasculitis) and disseminated intravascular coagulation.
   - Drug reactions: drug-induced immune complexes in vessel walls (hypersensitivity vasculitis).
   - Scurvy and Ehlers-Danlos syndrome [inherited collagen disorder with hyperextensible skin and joints]: collagen defects that weaken vessel walls.
   - Acquired vascular fragility in older adults; Cushing syndrome [glucocorticoid excess].
   - Henoch-Schönlein purpura [IgA vasculitis]: immune complex deposition in vessels.
   - Hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia: autosomal dominant; dilated, thin-walled vessels.
   - Perivascular amyloidosis, most common with AL amyloidosis.
2. **Platelet deficiency or dysfunction**
   - Decreased production: aplastic anaemia, leukaemia, certain drugs and alcohol, HIV, myelodysplastic neoplasm.
   - Decreased survival: immune (immune thrombocytopenic purpura, alloimmune, drug-induced) or nonimmune (DIC, thrombotic microangiopathies, mechanical injury from prosthetic heart valves).
   - Sequestration in an enlarged spleen.
   - Dilution after massive transfusion.
   - Platelet function defects from von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII] deficiency.
3. **Derangement of coagulation**
   - Inherited: haemophilia A, haemophilia B, von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor].
   - Acquired: vitamin K deficiency, severe parenchymal liver disease, DIC, and inhibitory autoantibodies.

## What do you mean by coagulation disorders?

**Coagulation disorders** are bleeding disorders caused by inherited or acquired deficiencies of coagulation factors; deficiencies of virtually every coagulation factor have been reported as causes of abnormal bleeding.
