Bleeding commonly manifests as large post-traumatic ecchymoses or haematomas, or prolonged bleeding from a laceration or after a surgical procedure. Unlike bleeding with thrombocytopenia, it often occurs in the gastrointestinal and urinary tracts and in weight-bearing joints (haemarthrosis).

## Classify coagulation disorders.

| Type | Features | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Inherited** | Typically affect a single clotting factor | Factor VIII deficiency (haemophilia A); factor IX deficiency (haemophilia B); von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII] deficiency (von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor]) |
| **Acquired** | Usually involve multiple factors, from decreased protein synthesis or a shortened protein half-life | Vitamin K deficiency (factors II, VII, IX, X and protein C); severe parenchymal liver disease; DIC, in which multiple factors are consumed |
| **Acquired, single factor** | Rare; caused by inhibitory autoantibodies | Autoantibody to a single clotting factor |

## What is bleeding time?

Bleeding time is a test of platelet function in which a standardised incision is made and the time taken for the bleeding to stop is measured.

- **Ivy method [bleeding time using a blood-pressure cuff and a standard skin incision]:** the most common; a cuff on the arm is inflated to 40 mm of mercury, a standardised device makes a small cut on the inner forearm, and the blood is blotted every 2 minutes until bleeding stops.
- **Duke method [bleeding time from a lancet puncture of the earlobe or fingertip]:** a lancet stab wound is made in a cleaned finger or earlobe, and timed in the same way.

It is an older test, time-consuming and difficult to standardise, which has been discarded.

## What is clotting time?

**Clotting time** is the time taken for blood to clot in a glass tube, the clotting being triggered by contact with the glass surface. It reflects the integrity of the intrinsic clotting pathway.

## Normal limits of bleeding time and clotting time.

| Test | Normal limit | Concerning (prolonged) |
|---|---|---|
| **Bleeding time, Duke method** [bleeding time from a lancet puncture of the earlobe or fingertip] | Under 3 minutes | Above 5 minutes |
| **Bleeding time, Ivy method** [bleeding time using a blood-pressure cuff and a standard skin incision] | Under 8 minutes | Above 10 minutes |
| **Clotting time** (whole blood, glass tube) | 9 to 15 minutes | Any defect in the intrinsic pathway prolongs it |

## Tests / screening tests / investigations for bleeding (haemorrhagic) disorders.

1. **Prothrombin time (PT):** assesses the extrinsic and common coagulation pathways. Clotting of plasma after addition of tissue thromboplastin and calcium is measured in seconds. Prolonged by deficiency or dysfunction of factor V, factor VII, factor X, prothrombin or fibrinogen.
2. **Partial thromboplastin time (PTT):** assesses the intrinsic and common pathways. Clotting of plasma after addition of kaolin, cephalin and calcium is measured in seconds. Prolonged by deficiency or dysfunction of factors V, VIII, IX, X, XI or XII, prothrombin or fibrinogen, or by interfering antiphospholipid antibodies.
3. **Platelet count:** reference range 150,000 to 350,000 per µL, by an electronic particle counter; confirm abnormal counts on a peripheral blood film, as clumping can cause spurious thrombocytopenia.
4. **Tests of platelet function:** platelet aggregation tests, and quantitative and qualitative tests of von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII], including the **ristocetin agglutination test**.
5. **Specific tests:** levels of specific clotting factors, fibrinogen, fibrin split products (particularly D-dimers) and circulating anticoagulants.
6. **Bleeding time:** an older test, time-consuming and difficult to standardise, which has been discarded.

## Two causes of raised prothrombin time.

1. **Vitamin K deficiency:** impairs the synthesis of factors II, VII, IX and X.
2. **Severe parenchymal liver disease:** inadequate synthesis of coagulation factors.

The prothrombin time is prolonged by deficiency or dysfunction of factor V, factor VII, factor X, prothrombin or fibrinogen.

## Normal findings in ITP, haemophilia and DIC in haemorrhagic screening tests.

| Disorder | Normal | Abnormal |
|---|---|---|
| **Immune thrombocytopenic purpura** | PT and PTT | Low platelet count (isolated thrombocytopenia) |
| **Haemophilia A and B** | PT | PTT prolonged |
| **Disseminated intravascular coagulation** | None stated | Consumption of platelets, fibrin and coagulation factors, with fibrin degradation products from fibrinolysis |

## What is von Willebrand factor?

**Von Willebrand factor** is a large protein that promotes the adhesion of platelets to subendothelial collagen and stabilises factor VIII.

- **It is made** by endothelial cells and by megakaryocytes, which are the source of the vWF in platelet alpha-granules.
- **It is secreted** as multimers containing as many as 100 subunits, which can exceed 20 million daltons in molecular mass.
- **Some** of the secreted vWF is deposited in the subendothelial matrix, ready to promote platelet adhesion if the endothelial lining is disrupted.

## Functions of von Willebrand factor.

1. **Promotes adhesion of platelets to the subendothelial matrix:** the most important haemostatic function; bridging interactions between platelet glycoprotein Ib/IX, vWF and matrix components such as collagen.
2. **Promotes platelet aggregation** by binding to activated glycoprotein IIb/IIIa integrins, of particular importance under high shear stress, such as in small vessels.
3. **Carries and stabilises factor VIII** in the circulation; the half-life of factor VIII rises from about 2.4 hours to about 12 hours.
4. Interacts with other proteins involved in haemostasis, including collagen and heparin.

## Why are both bleeding time and clotting time prolonged in von Willebrand disease?

- **Bleeding time (platelet function):** vWF promotes the adhesion of platelets to the subendothelial matrix, so patients with von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor] have defects in platelet function despite normal platelet counts; the level of active vWF, measured as ristocetin cofactor activity, is reduced.
- **Clotting time (intrinsic pathway):** vWF stabilises factor VIII in the circulation, so a deficiency of vWF lowers factor VIII; type 1 and type 3 disease are associated with a prolonged PTT.

## What is haemophilia?

**Haemophilia A** is the most common hereditary disease associated with life-threatening bleeding. It is caused by mutations in the gene encoding factor VIII, an essential cofactor for factor IX in the coagulation cascade, and is inherited as an X-linked recessive trait.

**Haemophilia B** (Christmas disease [haemophilia B, factor IX deficiency]) is an X-linked disorder caused by mutations in coagulation factor IX; it is clinically identical to haemophilia A.

## Classify haemophilia.

| Type | Deficient factor | Inheritance |
|---|---|---|
| **Haemophilia A** | Factor VIII | X-linked recessive |
| **Haemophilia B** (Christmas disease [haemophilia B, factor IX deficiency]) | Factor IX | X-linked recessive |

Haemophilia A is graded by the level of factor VIII activity:

| Severity | Factor VIII activity |
|---|---|
| Severe | Less than 1% of normal |
| Moderately severe | 2% to 5% of normal |
| Mild | 6% to 50% of normal |

## Give the laboratory diagnosis/findings of hemophilia.

1. **Prolonged PTT** and **normal PT**, pointing to an abnormality of the intrinsic coagulation pathway.
2. **Haemophilia A:** factor VIII-specific assays are required for diagnosis. Factor VIII function is measured by coagulation assays with mixtures of patient plasma and factor VIII-deficient plasma.
3. In severe haemophilia A from point mutations that impair function, factor VIII protein levels may be normal by immunoassay.
4. **Haemophilia B:** diagnosis is made by assay of factor IX levels; in about 15% of patients factor IX protein is present but nonfunctional.
5. About 15% of patients with severe haemophilia A develop antibodies that bind and inhibit factor VIII after treatment with recombinant factor VIII.

## What do you mean by DIC?

**Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC)** is an acute, subacute or chronic thrombohaemorrhagic disorder characterised by excessive activation of coagulation factors and the formation of thrombi in the microvasculature. It occurs as a secondary complication of many disorders.

The thrombotic diathesis leads to the consumption of platelets, fibrin and coagulation factors and, secondarily, activation of fibrinolysis.

## Give the laboratory diagnosis of DIC.

Diagnosis is based on clinical observation and laboratory studies:

1. Fibrinogen level, platelet count, PT and PTT: reflect the consumption of fibrinogen, platelets and clotting factors.
2. Fibrin degradation products, particularly D-dimers: produced when plasmin cleaves fibrin during fibrinolysis.
3. **Peripheral blood film:** fragmented red cells, from microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia.

## Write a short note on ITP.

**Definition**

Chronic immune thrombocytopenic purpura is caused by autoantibody-mediated destruction of platelets. It is secondary when it occurs with predisposing conditions such as systemic lupus erythematosus, HIV infection and B-cell neoplasms like chronic lymphocytic leukaemia, and primary (idiopathic) when no risk factor is known; primary ITP is diagnosed only after secondary causes are excluded.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Autoantibodies, most often against platelet membrane glycoproteins IIb/IIIa or Ib/IX, are found in the plasma and bound to platelets in about 80% of patients; most are IgG.
- The antibodies act as opsonins, recognised by IgG Fc receptors on phagocytes, so platelets are destroyed; the spleen is the major site, and splenectomy markedly improves the thrombocytopenia.
- Autoantibodies may also damage megakaryocytes, decreasing platelet production.

**Morphology**

- Spleen of normal size, with congested sinusoids, enlarged follicles with prominent reactive germinal centres, and scattered megakaryocytes in the sinuses.
- Marrow: modestly increased megakaryocytes, some immature with large, nonlobulated single nuclei.
- Blood: unusually large platelets (megathrombocytes).
- Petechial bleeds into skin and mucous membranes.

**Clinical features and complications**

- Most common in adult females younger than 40 years; female to male ratio 3 to 1; insidious onset.
- Bleeding into skin and mucosal surfaces: petechiae in dependent areas, ecchymoses, easy bruising, nosebleeds, gingival bleeding, soft tissue haemorrhage, melaena, haematuria and excessive menstrual flow.
- Subarachnoid and intracerebral haemorrhage are serious and sometimes fatal but rare in treated patients.
- No splenomegaly or lymphadenopathy in primary disease.
- Laboratory: low platelet count, normal or increased megakaryocytes, large platelets, normal PT and PTT.
- Treatment: glucocorticoids, splenectomy (normalises the count in about two-thirds), intravenous immunoglobulin, rituximab and thrombopoietin mimetics.
- **Acute ITP** is mainly a childhood disease, appearing abruptly 1 to 2 weeks after a viral illness and usually resolving spontaneously within 6 months.

## How are platelets destroyed in peripheral blood in ITP?

- Autoantibodies, most often against platelet membrane glycoproteins IIb/IIIa or Ib/IX, are bound to the platelet surface; in most cases they are of the IgG class.
- As in autoimmune haemolytic anaemias, the antiplatelet antibodies act as **opsonins**, recognised by IgG Fc receptors expressed on phagocytes, leading to increased platelet destruction.
- The **spleen** is the major site of removal of opsonised platelets, as shown by the marked improvement in thrombocytopenia after splenectomy.
- The splenic red pulp is also rich in plasma cells, a source of autoantibodies.
- The autoantibodies may also bind to and damage megakaryocytes, decreasing platelet production.

## How is ITP diagnosed in the laboratory?

1. **Platelet count low**, with normal or increased megakaryocytes in the bone marrow and large platelets (megathrombocytes) in the peripheral blood; these are presumptive evidence of accelerated platelet destruction.
2. **PT and PTT normal.**
3. **Bone marrow examination** is done to rule out thrombocytopenia due to marrow failure or other primary marrow disorders.
4. Tests for platelet autoantibodies have low sensitivity and specificity and are not clinically useful.
5. The diagnosis is one of **exclusion**: it is made only after other causes of thrombocytopenia have been ruled out, including secondary causes such as systemic lupus erythematosus, HIV infection and B-cell neoplasms.

## What are the causes of thrombocytopenia?

A platelet count below 150,000 per µL is thrombocytopenia. The causes fall into four categories.

1. **Decreased platelet production**
   - Conditions that depress marrow output generally: aplastic anaemia, leukaemia, disseminated cancer, granulomatous disease, megaloblastic anaemia from vitamin B12 or folate deficiency.
   - Conditions that affect megakaryocytes selectively: drugs (thiazides, cytotoxic drugs) and alcohol; infections such as measles and HIV; myelodysplastic neoplasm, which occasionally presents with isolated thrombocytopenia.
2. **Decreased platelet survival**
   - Immunologic: autoimmune (immune thrombocytopenic purpura, systemic lupus erythematosus, B-cell lymphoid neoplasms); alloimmune (post-transfusion and neonatal); drug-associated (quinidine, heparin, sulfa compounds); infections (HIV, infectious mononucleosis, dengue fever).
   - Nonimmunologic: **disseminated intravascular coagulation** and the thrombotic microangiopathies, in which platelet consumption reduces life span; mechanical injury, as with prosthetic heart valves.
3. **Sequestration:** the spleen normally sequesters 30% to 35% of the body's platelets, which can rise to 80% to 90% when the spleen is enlarged.
4. **Dilution:** massive transfusions.

## Classify bleeding (haemorrhagic) disorders.

| Group | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Increased fragility of vessels** | Infections (meningococcaemia, septicaemia, infective endocarditis, rickettsioses); drug reactions; scurvy and Ehlers-Danlos syndrome [inherited collagen disorder with hyperextensible skin and joints]; Cushing syndrome [glucocorticoid excess] and ageing; Henoch-Schönlein purpura [IgA vasculitis]; hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia; perivascular amyloidosis |
| **Platelet deficiency: thrombocytopenia** | Decreased production, decreased survival, sequestration, dilution; immune thrombocytopenic purpura; thrombotic microangiopathies (thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura, haemolytic uraemic syndrome) |
| **Platelet dysfunction** | Defects of adhesion (Bernard-Soulier syndrome [inherited platelet GPIb deficiency with giant platelets]), aggregation (Glanzmann thrombasthenia) and secretion (storage pool disorders); acquired: aspirin and other NSAIDs, uraemia |
| **Derangement of coagulation: inherited** | Haemophilia A (factor VIII), haemophilia B (factor IX), von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor] |
| **Derangement of coagulation: acquired** | Vitamin K deficiency, severe liver disease, disseminated intravascular coagulation, inhibitory autoantibodies |

## What screening tests are done for bleeding disorders (with interpretation)?

| Test | What it assesses | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| **Prothrombin time (PT)** | Extrinsic and common pathways | Prolonged in deficiency or dysfunction of factor V, VII, X, prothrombin or fibrinogen |
| **Partial thromboplastin time (PTT)** | Intrinsic and common pathways | Prolonged in deficiency or dysfunction of factors V, VIII, IX, X, XI, XII, prothrombin or fibrinogen, or by antiphospholipid antibodies |
| **Platelet count** | Platelet number; reference range 150,000 to 350,000 per µL | Less than 150,000 per µL is thrombocytopenia; 20,000 to 50,000 aggravates bleeding after trauma; less than 20,000 may cause spontaneous bleeding; confirm on a blood film, since clumping gives spurious thrombocytopenia |
| **Tests of platelet function** | Platelet aggregation; quantitative and qualitative tests of von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII] | Used when the count is normal but platelet function is suspected |
| **Specific tests** | Individual clotting factors, fibrinogen, fibrin split products (D-dimers), circulating anticoagulants | Used to pin down the defect |

Bleeding time is an older test, now discarded because it is time-consuming and difficult to standardise.

**Pattern in common disorders**
- Vessel wall abnormalities: platelet count, PT and PTT normal.
- Isolated thrombocytopenia: low platelet count; PT and PTT normal.
- Haemophilia A and B: prolonged PTT; normal PT.
- Von Willebrand disease: reduced ristocetin cofactor activity; PTT prolonged in types 1 and 3.
- DIC: fibrinogen, platelets, PT, PTT and fibrin degradation products (D-dimers) are measured.

## Give the normal findings and expected changes in ITP, hemophilia and DIC.

| Disorder | Normal | Abnormal |
|---|---|---|
| **Immune thrombocytopenic purpura** | PT and PTT | Low platelet count (isolated thrombocytopenia) |
| **Haemophilia A and B** | PT | PTT prolonged |
| **Disseminated intravascular coagulation** | None stated | Consumption of platelets, fibrin and coagulation factors; fibrin degradation products, particularly D-dimers, raised |

## What are the causes of gum bleeding?

1. **Vessel wall abnormalities:** small haemorrhages in the skin or mucous membranes, particularly the gingivae. Causes include infections (meningococcaemia, septicaemia, infective endocarditis), drug reactions, scurvy, Ehlers-Danlos syndrome [inherited collagen disorder with hyperextensible skin and joints], Henoch-Schönlein purpura [IgA vasculitis], hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia and perivascular amyloidosis.
2. **Chronic immune thrombocytopenic purpura:** easy bruising, nosebleeds and gingival bleeding from minor trauma.
3. **Thrombocytopenia:** spontaneous bleeding from small vessels, in the skin and mucous membranes.
4. **Coagulation factor deficiencies:** prolonged bleeding after a tooth extraction.

## Define thrombocytopenia.

Reduction in platelet number is called **thrombocytopenia**. A count less than 150,000 platelets per µL is generally considered to constitute thrombocytopenia.

## Discuss in short the pathogenesis of ITP.

Chronic immune thrombocytopenic purpura is caused by **autoantibody-mediated destruction of platelets**.

1. Autoantibodies, most often against platelet membrane glycoproteins **IIb/IIIa** or **Ib/IX**, are found in the plasma and bound to platelets in about 80% of patients.
2. They are mostly of the IgG class.
3. As in autoimmune haemolytic anaemias, the antibodies act as **opsonins**, recognised by IgG Fc receptors on phagocytes, which leads to increased platelet destruction.
4. The **spleen** is the major site of removal of opsonised platelets, so splenectomy markedly improves the thrombocytopenia.
5. The splenic red pulp is rich in plasma cells, so splenectomy may also remove a source of autoantibodies.
6. The autoantibodies may also bind and damage megakaryocytes, which decreases platelet production.

Chronic ITP may be secondary (systemic lupus erythematosus, HIV infection, B-cell neoplasms such as chronic lymphocytic leukaemia) or primary (idiopathic). In acute ITP the autoantibodies appear 1 to 2 weeks after a self-limited viral illness, by uncertain mechanisms.

## Give the blood and bone marrow picture of ITP.

**Blood**
- Low platelet count (isolated thrombocytopenia).
- Unusually large platelets (**megathrombocytes**), a sign of accelerated thrombopoiesis.
- PT and PTT normal.

**Bone marrow**
- Modestly increased number of megakaryocytes; some are immature, with large nonlobulated single nuclei.
- These findings reflect accelerated thrombopoiesis and are not specific.
- Marrow examination is important to rule out thrombocytopenia due to marrow failure or other primary marrow disorders.

## A child has purpuric rash/gum bleeding, thrombocytopenia, normocellular marrow with active megakaryopoiesis. What is the likely diagnosis?

**Immune thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP).**
- Purpura and gingival bleeding point to bleeding from a low platelet count.
- A low platelet count with normal or increased megakaryocytes in the marrow is taken as presumptive evidence of accelerated platelet destruction.
- Acute ITP is mainly a disease of childhood, with abrupt onset, often 1 to 2 weeks after a viral illness, and it is self-limited, usually resolving within 6 months.
- Diagnosis is one of exclusion, after other causes of thrombocytopenia are ruled out.

## Why is APTT prolonged in hemophilia?

- Haemophilia A is caused by mutations in the gene for factor VIII, an essential cofactor for factor IX in the coagulation cascade.
- The factor VIIIa and factor IXa complex has the preeminent role in activation of factor X.
- Factor VIII deficiency therefore causes an abnormality of the **intrinsic coagulation pathway**.
- The PTT assesses the intrinsic and common pathways, so it is **prolonged**; the PT, which assesses the extrinsic and common pathways, is normal.

## Write a short note on von Willebrand disease.

**Definition.** Autosomal dominant disorder caused by mutations in von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII] (vWF), a large protein that promotes the adhesion of platelets to subendothelial collagen and stabilises factor VIII.

**Factor VIII and vWF**
- Factor VIII is a cofactor of factor IX; vWF is made by endothelial cells and megakaryocytes.
- vWF binds and stabilises factor VIII, raising its half-life from about 2.4 hours to about 12 hours.
- vWF promotes platelet adhesion to the subendothelial matrix, by bridging platelet glycoprotein Ib/IX and collagen.

**Types**
1. **Type 1:** autosomal dominant; mild to moderate quantitative deficiency of vWF; about 70% of cases; incomplete penetrance and variable expressivity; generally mild.
2. **Type 2:** autosomal dominant; qualitative defect with missense mutations that cause defective multimer assembly, so large and intermediate multimers are missing; 25% of cases; type 2A is commonest; mild to moderate bleeding.
3. **Type 3:** autosomal recessive; deletions or frameshift mutations of both alleles, with little or no vWF; factor VIII is also reduced; severe bleeding.

**Clinical and laboratory features**
- Mild to moderate bleeding disorder resembling that of thrombocytopenia.
- Defective platelet function with a normal platelet count.
- Reduced ristocetin cofactor activity.
- Prolonged PTT in types 1 and 3.

**Treatment.** Desmopressin, which stimulates vWF release, or plasma concentrates containing factor VIII and vWF, or recombinant vWF, for types 1 and 2; prophylactic plasma concentrates for type 3.

## Write a short note on hemophilia (A) / its characteristic features.

**Definition and aetiology**
- The most common hereditary disease associated with life-threatening bleeding.
- Caused by mutations in the gene encoding **factor VIII**, an essential cofactor for factor IX.
- Inherited as an **X-linked recessive** trait; affects mainly males and homozygous females.
- About 30% of patients have no family history and have new mutations.

**Severity, by factor VIII activity**
- Less than 1% of normal: severe.
- 2% to 5%: moderately severe.
- 6% to 50%: mild.
- The most severe deficiencies result from an inversion involving the X chromosome that abolishes synthesis of factor VIII.

**Clinical features**
- Easy bruising and massive haemorrhage after trauma or surgery.
- Spontaneous haemorrhage into joints (**haemarthroses**) and other regions susceptible to trauma; recurrent bleeding causes progressive disabling deformities.
- Petechiae are characteristically absent.

**Laboratory**
- Prolonged PTT and normal PT.
- Factor VIII-specific assays are required for diagnosis.

**Treatment**
- Recombinant factor VIII, or a bispecific antibody linking factor IXa and factor Xa.
- About 15% of patients with severe disease develop antibodies that inhibit factor VIII.

## Why is hemophilia called an X-linked recessive disorder?

- Haemophilia A is caused by mutations in the gene encoding factor VIII, which is carried on the X chromosome.
- It is inherited as an **X-linked recessive** trait.
- It therefore affects mainly **males** and homozygous females.
- Rarely, heterozygous females bleed excessively, presumably because the X chromosome bearing the normal factor VIII allele is inactivated by chance in most cells (unfavourable lyonisation).
- About 30% of patients have no family history; the disease is caused by new mutations.

## A boy has hemarthrosis after a small fall; maternal uncle bled after tooth extraction. What is your provisional diagnosis?

**Haemophilia A** (factor VIII deficiency), an X-linked recessive disorder.

- Haemarthrosis after minor stress to a joint and prolonged bleeding after a tooth extraction are typical of bleeding due to coagulation factor deficiency.
- The affected maternal uncle fits X-linked recessive inheritance, which affects mainly males.
- Haemophilia B (factor IX deficiency) is clinically indistinguishable, so factor VIII and factor IX assays are needed to confirm.
- PTT is prolonged and PT is normal.

## Same: what type of disease is it?

- An inherited bleeding disorder due to deficiency of a coagulation factor, factor VIII in haemophilia A.
- **X-linked recessive**: it affects mainly males and homozygous females.

## Describe the pathogenesis of DIC.

DIC is not a primary disease but an acquired coagulopathy that occurs in the course of many clinical conditions. It results from pathological activation of coagulation or impairment of clot-inhibiting mechanisms.

**Two major triggers**
1. Release of **tissue factor** or other procoagulants into the circulation.
   - Sources: placenta in obstetric complications; tissues injured by trauma or burns.
   - Mucus from certain adenocarcinomas can activate factor X directly.
2. Widespread **endothelial injury**.
   - Endothelial cell necrosis exposes the subendothelial matrix, which activates platelets and the coagulation pathway.
   - **TNF** in sepsis makes endothelial cells express tissue factor and reduces thrombomodulin, and increases adhesion molecules, so leucocytes release reactive oxygen species and proteases.
   - Other causes: antigen-antibody complexes (systemic lupus erythematosus), temperature extremes (heat stroke, burns), and microorganisms (meningococci, rickettsiae).

**Associated conditions.** Obstetric complications, malignant neoplasms, sepsis and major trauma; among cancers, acute promyelocytic leukaemia and adenocarcinomas of the lung, pancreas, colon and stomach.

**Consequences**
1. Widespread deposition of fibrin in the microcirculation, causing ischaemia of organs and **microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia**.
2. Consumption of platelets and clotting factors and activation of plasminogen, causing a **haemorrhagic diathesis**.

## Explain why bleeding occurs in DIC.

1. The thrombotic diathesis consumes **platelets, fibrin and coagulation factors**.
2. Fibrinolysis is activated: plasmin cleaves fibrin and also digests factors V and VIII, reducing their concentration further.
3. Fibrin degradation products inhibit platelet aggregation, fibrin polymerisation and thrombin.
4. This depletion of the factors required for haemostasis, with activated fibrinolysis, causes bleeding.

Acute DIC, for example with obstetric complications or major trauma, is dominated by a bleeding diathesis.

## Classify blood groups.

Blood groups are classified by the red cell antigens that define them. The two systems of main clinical importance are:

| System | Groups | Basis |
|---|---|---|
| **ABO** | A, B, AB and O | Presence of antigen A, antigen B, both, or neither on the red cells |
| **Rh** | Rh positive and Rh negative | Presence or absence of the D antigen on the red cells |

Other red cell antigens, such as Kell [red cell blood group system] and Kidd [red cell blood group system], can cause delayed haemolytic transfusion reactions.

## Name the ABO and Rh blood groups with their agglutinins.

| Blood group | Antigen on red cells | Agglutinin (antibody) in serum |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | A | Anti-B |
| **B** | B | Anti-A |
| **AB** | A and B | Neither anti-A nor anti-B |
| **O** | Neither A nor B | Anti-A and anti-B |
| **Rh positive** | D antigen present | No naturally occurring anti-D |
| **Rh negative** | D antigen absent | Anti-D formed only after exposure to D-positive red cells, by transfusion or foetal red cells in pregnancy |

The anti-A and anti-B antibodies occur naturally, mainly IgM and IgG; anti-D is an immune antibody, mainly IgG.

## What do you mean by cross matching?

**Cross matching** is the final check that a specific donor unit is compatible with a specific recipient. It tests ABO compatibility and whether the recipient has an unexpected antibody that reacts with donor red cells.


## How is cross matching performed?

1. **Major crossmatch** (standard test): donor red cells are mixed with recipient serum or plasma.
2. **Immediate spin phase:** donor red cells in saline are added to the patient serum, mixed and centrifuged for 15 to 30 seconds; agglutination or **haemolysis** is read (detects ABO incompatibility only; misses most IgG antibodies such as anti-D, anti-K, anti-Jka, anti-Fya).
3. **37°C incubation phase:** donor red cells in a potentiating agent are added to the patient serum and incubated at 37°C.
4. **Antiglobulin phase:** the cells are washed, **antihuman globulin** (Coombs [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells] reagent) is added, the mixture is centrifuged for 15 to 30 seconds and read for agglutination; agglutination means patient IgG is bound to the donor antigen.
5. **Minor crossmatch:** donor plasma with recipient cells; largely obsolete for packed red cells because little donor plasma remains.
6. **Electronic crossmatch** is allowed only if all four conditions are met:
- Two ABO determinations, one on the current specimen
- Current antibody screen negative
- No history of a clinically significant unexpected antibody
- A validated on-site computer system that blocks release of ABO-incompatible units
7. Specimen is collected within 3 days of transfusion, or within the last 3 months if the patient is pregnant.


## Why is cross matching essential for blood transfusion?

- Confirms serologically that the donor unit is **ABO compatible** with the recipient.
- Detects **unexpected red cell antibodies** in the recipient that react with the donor cells.
- Prevents **acute haemolytic reaction**, caused by preformed IgM antibodies against donor red cells that fix complement; it usually results from an ABO-incompatible unit given through an error in patient identification or tube labelling.
- The immediate spin detects ABO incompatibility only; the antiglobulin phase is needed to detect IgG antibodies.


## Mention 4 important hematological changes of stored blood.

1. **Fall in pH:** lactate accumulates; pH falls from 7.2 on day 0 to 6.54 on day 35 (CPDA-1 blood).
2. **Rise in plasma potassium:** about eight-fold rise; 4.33 mmol/L on day 0 to 33.05 mmol/L on day 35 (CPDA-1 blood).
3. **Depletion of 2,3-DPG:** falls to zero in about 2 weeks; the oxygen dissociation curve shifts to the left; it is regenerated within 24 hours of transfusion.
4. **Fall in ATP:** rises over days 1 to 7, then falls from day 7 onwards.
5. **Increased haemolysis:** 0.09% on day 0 to 0.68% on day 35 (CPDA-1 blood); plasma haemoglobin increases and sodium decreases.
6. **Loss of platelets and labile factors:** platelets lose viability and function very quickly; factor VIII deteriorates rapidly, especially after the first 24 hours; factor V is labile.
7. At expiry, at least 75% of transfused red cells must remain in circulation 24 hours after transfusion.


## What are the indications of blood transfusion?

| Component | Indications |
|---|---|
| **Red cells** | Haemoglobin below 7.0 g/dL (target 8.0 g/dL in cardiac disease); haemoglobin below 100 g/L with high risk of myocardial ischaemia; major active bleeding |
| **Platelets** | Count below 10,000 per microlitre; below 20,000 with fever, neutropenia or risk of intracranial haemorrhage; below 50,000 with active bleeding or an invasive procedure; below 80,000 for neurosurgery or eye surgery; platelet function defects with bleeding |
| **Fresh frozen plasma** | INR above 1.5 with an invasive procedure or with active bleeding; DIC with bleeding; liver disease with bleeding; massive haemorrhage; warfarin reversal in life-threatening bleeding; plasma exchange for TTP |
| **Cryoprecipitate** | Active bleeding with fibrinogen below 1.5 g/L; DIC with significant bleeding and fibrinogen below 1.0 g/L; hereditary hypofibrinogenaemia, haemophilia and von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor] |


## Blood components used in transfusion.

| Component | Use | Storage |
|---|---|---|
| **Whole blood** | Massive transfusion in trauma or shock; exchange transfusion | Must be ABO identical; platelets non-functional and labile factors lost |
| **Packed red cells** | Symptomatic anaemia and surgical blood loss | 1 to 6°C; 35 days (CPDA-1), 42 days (additive solution) |
| **Platelet concentrate** | Prevent or treat bleeding in thrombocytopenia | 20 to 24°C with constant agitation; 5 days for a single unit |
| **Fresh frozen plasma** | Replaces labile and stable clotting factors | At or below minus 18°C for 12 months; after thawing 24 hours at 1 to 6°C |
| **Cryoprecipitate** | Source of factor VIII, fibrinogen and von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII]; haemophilia, von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor], DIC, low fibrinogen | At or below minus 18°C for 1 year |
| **Granulocytes** | Neutropenic patients with culture-proven gram-negative infection unresponsive to antibiotics | 20 to 24°C for 24 hours; infused as soon as possible |


## Screening tests for safe blood transfusion / diseases for which blood must be screened.

1. **HIV-1 and HIV-2:** anti-HIV-1/2 immunoassay, or preferably a combined p24 antigen and antibody immunoassay.
2. **Hepatitis B:** HBsAg immunoassay.
3. **Hepatitis C:** anti-HCV immunoassay, or preferably a combined antigen and antibody assay.
4. **Syphilis:** treponemal antibody test (TPHA or EIA).
5. **Malaria:** thick film or antigen EIA in endemic areas (selective).
6. **Chagas disease [Trypanosoma cruzi infection], HTLV-I/II and CMV:** antibody tests where indicated (selective).
7. ABO and RhD grouping is done on all donations; only units non-reactive on all tests are released.


## Contraindications of blood transfusion.

- **Absolute:** patient refusal, such as religious objection.
- Transfusion should be avoided in a stable patient with haemoglobin 7 to 8 g/dL, who is not expected to benefit.
- Iron deficiency without haemodynamic instability calls for iron therapy first.
- Plasma is not given for a mildly raised INR (below 1.8) or routine non-emergent warfarin reversal; vitamin K is preferred.
- Severe IgA deficiency needs washed cells or platelets, or plasma from IgA-deficient donors.
- Patients at risk of circulatory overload need slow infusion of red cells with prophylactic diuretics.
- Post-transfusion purpura: avoid further platelet or red cell transfusion.


## Mention the diseases transmitted by blood transfusion.

1. **Viruses:** HIV, hepatitis C and hepatitis B; estimated rates are 1 in 1.5 million, 1 in 1.2 million and 1 in 1 million units respectively; also West Nile virus, HTLV-I/II and CMV.
2. **Bacteria:** mostly skin flora contaminating the unit at collection; symptomatic contamination is much more common with platelets (as high as 1 in 5000) than with red cells, because platelets are stored at room temperature.
3. **Syphilis**
4. **Protozoa:** malaria, Chagas disease [Trypanosoma cruzi infection] (trypanosomes) and babesia.
5. Emerging agents: variant CJD, dengue, chikungunya.


## Hazards / immediate and delayed complications of blood transfusion.

| Reaction | Features |
|---|---|
| **Febrile nonhaemolytic** | Commonest; fever and chills, sometimes mild dyspnoea, within 6 hours of red cell or platelet transfusion; due to inflammatory mediators from donor leucocytes; frequency rises with storage age; responds to antipyretics |
| **Allergic** | Severe reactions in sensitised recipients, most likely in IgA deficiency (about 1 in 600), where IgG antibodies recognise IgA; occur in 1 in 20,000 to 1 in 50,000 transfusions; urticarial reactions are IgE mediated, occur in 1% to 3% and are mild |
| **Acute haemolytic** | Preformed IgM antibodies against donor red cells fix complement; usually ABO-incompatible unit; fever, shaking chills and flank pain; may progress to DIC, shock, acute renal failure and death |
| **TRALI** | Transfusion-related acute lung injury; sudden respiratory failure during or soon after transfusion; bilateral pulmonary infiltrates not responding to diuretics; fever, hypotension and hypoxaemia; incidence less than 1 in 10,000; mortality 5% in uncomplicated cases, up to 67% in the severely ill |
| **Bacterial infection** | Fever, chills and hypotension; more common with platelets |
| **Circulatory overload** | About 1% of transfusions; respiratory distress or hypertension within 12 hours |
| **Delayed haemolytic** | IgG antibodies to antigens of previous sensitisation; extravascular haemolysis; positive direct Coombs test [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells], low haptoglobin, raised LDH; antibodies to Rh, Kell [red cell blood group system] and Kidd [red cell blood group system] may cause severe reactions; occurs after 24 hours, usually about 2 weeks and up to 30 days |
| **Viral infection** | HIV, hepatitis C and hepatitis B |
| **Graft-versus-host disease** | 4 to 30 days after transfusion |
| **Post-transfusion purpura** | 5 to 10 days after transfusion |
| **Iron overload** | Generally after 15 to 20 red cell units |


## Write a short note on Rh-incompatibility.

**Definition.** Immune hydrops is a haemolytic disease caused by blood group antigen incompatibility between mother and foetus; it occurs in second and subsequent pregnancies of an Rh-negative mother with an Rh-positive father.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**
- The foetus inherits red cell antigens from the father that are foreign to the mother.
- Foetal red cells reach the maternal circulation in the last trimester or during childbirth, and the mother becomes sensitised.
- The first exposure evokes IgM antibodies, which do not cross the placenta; so Rh disease is uncommon in the first pregnancy.
- Exposure in a later pregnancy gives a brisk IgG response; the antibodies cross the placenta and lyse foetal red cells.
- Of the Rh antigens, only the **D antigen** is a major cause.
- Haemolytic disease develops only after a significant transplacental bleed (more than 1 mL of Rh-positive foetal red cells).
- Concurrent ABO incompatibility protects the mother, because foetal red cells are promptly coated and removed by anti-A or anti-B IgM antibodies.

**Morphology**
- Foetus and placenta are pale; liver and spleen are enlarged.
- Bone marrow shows compensatory hyperplasia of erythroid precursors, and extramedullary haematopoiesis is present in liver, spleen and lymph nodes.
- Many immature red cells (reticulocytes and erythroblasts) circulate, called **erythroblastosis fetalis**.
- **Kernicterus:** brain is enlarged, oedematous and bright yellow from bilirubin, especially in the basal ganglia, thalamus, cerebellum, cerebral grey matter and spinal cord; usually needs bilirubin above 20 mg/dL in term infants.

**Clinical features and complications**
- Anaemia, with hypoxic injury to the heart and liver; cardiac failure and fall in plasma proteins lead to generalised oedema and anasarca (**hydrops fetalis**).
- Unconjugated bilirubin passes through the infant's poorly developed blood-brain barrier and causes jaundice and kernicterus.
- Mild cases show pallor, with hepatosplenomegaly; severe cases show intense jaundice, generalised oedema and neurological injury.
- Infants may be stillborn, die in the first days, or recover completely.
- Treatment: phototherapy, exchange transfusion; foetal intravascular transfusion and early delivery in severe intrauterine haemolysis.
- Prevention: Rhesus immune globulin (anti-D) to Rh-negative mothers at 28 weeks and within 72 hours of delivery, and after abortions.


## Complications of mismatched blood transfusion.

- Cause: preformed IgM antibodies against donor red cells fix complement; most commonly an ABO-incompatible unit given through an error in patient identification or tube labelling.
- **Intravascular haemolysis** and haemoglobinuria.
- Fever, shaking chills and flank pain appear rapidly; direct Coombs test [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells] is typically positive.
- Signs and symptoms are due to complement activation rather than intravascular haemolysis itself.
- Free haemoglobin causes **acute kidney injury** with reduced urine output.
- **DIC**, with diffuse bleeding.
- Shock and acute renal failure.
- Death in severe cases.


## What is an anticoagulant?

An **anticoagulant** is a chemical substance that prevents blood from clotting. In the laboratory it is added to blood in a fixed proportion and used only in vitro.


## Enumerate anticoagulants used in the laboratory.

1. **EDTA** (purple cap): chelates calcium.
2. **Trisodium citrate** (light blue cap): binds calcium reversibly.
3. **Heparin** (green cap): binds antithrombin, which inactivates thrombin and other clotting factors.
4. **Oxalates:** double oxalate (potassium and ammonium oxalate in the ratio 2 to 3, at 2 mg/mL) or potassium oxalate; precipitate calcium as insoluble calcium oxalate.
5. **Sodium fluoride:** used with oxalate for glucose estimation.
6. **ACD, CPD and CPDA-1:** used for blood storage.


## Merits and demerits of anticoagulants.

| Anticoagulant | Merits | Demerits |
|---|---|---|
| **EDTA** | Preserves cell morphology for about 3 hours; inhibits platelet clumping; suits PCR | Unsuitable for coagulation studies (binds calcium irreversibly, affects factors V and VIII); causes platelet satellitism; excess shrinks cells and lowers PCV; inhibits ALP and CK |
| **Trisodium citrate** | Binding of calcium is reversible; preserves factors V and VIII | Liquid dilutes the blood, so unsuitable for PCV, haemoglobin, TLC and DLC; inhibits ALP and aminotransferase |
| **Heparin** | Does not alter red cell size; reduces lysis | Effect is temporary; causes platelet and leucocyte clumping, so not for counts or films; bluish background on films; inhibits PCR |
| **Oxalates** | Used for chemistry tests, PCV, ESR, TLC and specific gravity | Potassium oxalate shrinks red cells; ammonium oxalate swells them |
| **Sodium fluoride** | Preserves glucose | Inhibits many enzymes; interferes with electrolyte measurement; poorly preserves white cell morphology |


## Name the blood groups with their antigens.

| Blood group | Antigen on red cells | Antibodies in plasma |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | A | Anti-B |
| **B** | B | Anti-A |
| **AB** | A and B | Neither anti-A nor anti-B |
| **O** | Neither A nor B | Anti-A and anti-B |
| **Rh positive** | D antigen present | No naturally occurring anti-D |
| **Rh negative** | D antigen absent | Anti-D formed only after exposure to D-positive red cells |

## What are the commonly used blood products?

| Product | Use |
|---|---|
| **Whole blood** | Massive transfusion in trauma or shock; exchange transfusion |
| **Packed red cells** | Symptomatic anaemia and surgical blood loss |
| **Platelet concentrate** | Prevent or treat bleeding in thrombocytopenia |
| **Fresh frozen plasma** | Replaces labile and stable clotting factors |
| **Cryoprecipitate** | Source of factor VIII, fibrinogen and von Willebrand factor [platelet-adhesion glycoprotein that carries factor VIII]; haemophilia, von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor], DIC, low fibrinogen |
| **Granulocytes** | Neutropenic patients with culture-proven gram-negative infection unresponsive to antibiotics |

## What are the hazards/complications of blood transfusion?

| Reaction | Features |
|---|---|
| **Febrile nonhaemolytic** | Commonest; fever and chills, sometimes mild dyspnoea, within 6 hours of red cell or platelet transfusion; due to inflammatory mediators from donor leucocytes; responds to antipyretics |
| **Allergic** | Severe reactions in sensitised recipients, most likely in IgA deficiency (about 1 in 600), where IgG antibodies recognise IgA; occur in 1 in 20,000 to 1 in 50,000 transfusions; urticarial reactions are IgE mediated, occur in 1% to 3% and are mild |
| **Acute haemolytic** | Preformed IgM antibodies against donor red cells fix complement; usually an ABO-incompatible unit; fever, shaking chills and flank pain; may progress to DIC, shock, acute renal failure and death |
| **Transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI)** | Sudden respiratory failure during or soon after transfusion; bilateral pulmonary infiltrates not responding to diuretics; fever, hypotension and hypoxaemia; incidence less than 1 in 10,000; mortality 5% in uncomplicated cases, up to 67% in the severely ill |
| **Bacterial infection** | Fever, chills and hypotension; more common with platelets |
| **Circulatory overload** | About 1% of transfusions; respiratory distress or hypertension within 12 hours |
| **Delayed haemolytic** | IgG antibodies to antigens of previous sensitisation; extravascular haemolysis; positive direct Coombs test [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells], low haptoglobin, raised lactate dehydrogenase; antibodies to Rh, Kell [red cell blood group system] and Kidd [red cell blood group system] may cause severe reactions |
| **Viral infection** | HIV, hepatitis C and hepatitis B; estimated 1 in 1.5 million, 1 in 1.2 million and 1 in 1 million units |
| **Graft-versus-host disease** | 4 to 30 days after transfusion |
| **Post-transfusion purpura** | 5 to 10 days after transfusion |
| **Iron overload** | Generally after 15 to 20 red cell units |

## Write a short note on mismatched blood transfusion (adverse effects).

**Cause.** An error in patient identification or tube labelling that allows a patient to receive an ABO-incompatible unit of blood.

**Pathogenesis**
- Preformed high-affinity natural **IgM** antibodies, usually against the polysaccharide blood group antigens A or B, bind the donor red cells.
- They fix complement and rapidly induce complement-mediated lysis, **intravascular haemolysis** and haemoglobinuria.
- The signs and symptoms are due to complement activation rather than intravascular haemolysis itself.

**Adverse effects**
1. Fever, shaking chills and flank pain, appearing rapidly.
2. Haemoglobinuria.
3. The direct Coombs test [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells] is typically positive, unless all the donor red cells have lysed.
4. In severe cases the process rapidly progresses to **DIC**, **shock**, **acute renal failure** and occasionally death.

**Delayed haemolytic reactions**
- Caused by IgG antibodies to red cell antigens to which the recipient was sensitised earlier, for example by a prior transfusion.
- Extravascular haemolysis, with a positive direct Coombs test, low haptoglobin and raised lactate dehydrogenase.
- Antibodies to Rh, Kell [red cell blood group system] and Kidd [red cell blood group system] antigens may cause severe and potentially fatal reactions.

## Name the immediate blood transfusion reactions.

1. **Febrile nonhaemolytic reaction:** fever and chills, sometimes with mild dyspnoea, within 6 hours.
2. **Allergic reactions:** urticarial, or severe in IgA-deficient recipients.
3. **Acute haemolytic reaction:** fever, shaking chills and flank pain.
4. Transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI): sudden respiratory failure during or soon after transfusion.
5. **Bacterial infection:** fever, chills and hypotension.
6. **Circulatory overload:** respiratory distress or hypertension within 12 hours.

## What is hemolytic transfusion reaction?

- **Acute haemolytic reaction:** preformed IgM antibodies against donor red cells fix complement; usually after an ABO-incompatible unit is given through an error in patient identification or labelling; intravascular haemolysis, haemoglobinuria, fever, shaking chills and flank pain.
- **Delayed haemolytic reaction:** IgG antibodies against red cell antigens to which the recipient was sensitised previously; extravascular haemolysis with a positive direct Coombs test [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells], low haptoglobin and raised lactate dehydrogenase.

## Write a short note on preservation of blood.

**Collection**
- About 450 mL of blood is collected into a bag containing 63 mL of anticoagulant-preservative solution, usually **citrate phosphate dextrose adenine (CPDA-1)**.
- Citrate chelates calcium and prevents clotting.
- Phosphate maintains the pH and the 2,3-DPG level.
- Dextrose is the energy source for red cell metabolism.
- Adenine is the substrate for ATP synthesis, and extends the shelf life.

**Storage**
- Whole blood and red cells are kept at 1°C to 6°C.
- Shelf life is 21 days in ACD and CPD, 35 days in CPDA-1 and 42 days with additive solutions.
- Platelet concentrates are kept at 20°C to 24°C with constant agitation, for 5 days.
- Fresh frozen plasma and cryoprecipitate are kept at or below minus 18°C, for 12 months and 1 year.

**Changes during storage (storage lesion)**
1. Fall in pH.
2. Rise in plasma potassium.
3. Depletion of 2,3-DPG, which shifts the oxygen dissociation curve to the left; it is regenerated within 24 hours of transfusion.
4. Fall in ATP after day 7.
5. Increased haemolysis.
6. Loss of platelets and of the labile factors V and VIII.
7. At expiry, at least 75% of transfused red cells must remain in circulation 24 hours after transfusion.

## Name the commonly used anticoagulants with advantages (incl. those in blood bag).

**For laboratory samples**

| Anticoagulant | Advantages |
|---|---|
| **EDTA** | Preserves cell morphology for about 3 hours; inhibits platelet clumping; suits PCR |
| **Trisodium citrate** | Binding of calcium is reversible, so factors V and VIII are preserved |
| **Heparin** | Does not alter red cell size; reduces lysis |
| **Double oxalate** | Used for chemistry tests, PCV, ESR and total leucocyte count |
| **Sodium fluoride** | Preserves glucose |

**For blood bags**

| Solution | Advantages |
|---|---|
| **ACD (acid citrate dextrose)** | Citrate prevents clotting; dextrose supplies energy; storage for 21 days |
| **CPD (citrate phosphate dextrose)** | Phosphate maintains pH and 2,3-DPG; storage for 21 days |
| **CPDA-1** | Adenine supports ATP synthesis; storage for 35 days |
| **Additive solutions** | Red cells stored for 42 days |

## DIC picture after delivery or induced abortion — Probable diagnosis? Tests / laboratory investigations to establish it (with results)?

**Probable diagnosis:** disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), an acquired coagulopathy and not a primary disease. Obstetric conditions are among the commonest triggers; procoagulants derived from the placenta, dead retained foetus or amniotic fluid enter the circulation.

**Investigations**

Diagnosis is based on clinical observation and laboratory studies:

| Test | Result |
|---|---|
| Platelet count | Reduced, from consumption of platelets |
| Fibrinogen | Reduced, from consumption of fibrin and clotting factors |
| Prothrombin time (PT) and partial thromboplastin time (PTT) | Prolonged, from depletion of clotting factors; plasmin also digests factors V and VIII |
| Fibrin degradation products, particularly D-dimers | Raised, from activation of fibrinolysis |
| Peripheral blood film | Fragmented red cells of microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia |


## Child with severe anaemia, mongoloid facies, splenomegaly/hepatosplenomegaly ± jaundice, short stature, microcytic hypochromic picture with target cells/normoblasts/fragmented cells — Probable / provisional diagnosis? How will you confirm it?

**Probable diagnosis:** β-thalassaemia major.

**Basis**

- Anaemia manifests 6 to 9 months after birth as haemoglobin synthesis switches from HbF to HbA; untransfused haemoglobin is 3 to 6 g/dL.
- Marrow expansion in facial bones and skull gives the facial change.
- Hepatosplenomegaly from phagocyte hyperplasia and extramedullary haematopoiesis; the spleen may weigh up to 1500 g.
- Growth retardation in untreated children.

**Confirmation**

1. **Blood smear:** anisocytosis, poikilocytosis, microcytosis and hypochromia; target cells, basophilic stippling and fragmented red cells; nucleated red cell precursors (normoblasts).
2. **Reticulocyte count:** raised, but lower than expected for the severity of the anaemia, because of ineffective erythropoiesis.
3. **Haemoglobin analysis:** HbA absent (β0/β0) or present in small amounts (β+/β+ or β0/β+); HbF is the major haemoglobin and is markedly elevated.
4. **Marrow:** striking expansion of haematopoietically active marrow; "crewcut" appearance of the skull on X-ray.
5. **Iron studies:** to separate from iron deficiency; iron overload (haemosiderosis) occurs in transfused patients.


## 9-year-old / 7-year-old girl with sudden purpuric rash, thrombocytopenia, normocellular marrow with active megakaryopoiesis — Likely diagnosis? Further investigations for confirmation?

**Likely diagnosis:** acute immune thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP).

**Basis**

- Acute ITP is mainly a disease of childhood.
- Symptoms appear abruptly, often 1 to 2 weeks after a self-limited viral illness.
- It is caused by autoantibodies to platelets.
- It is self-limited, usually resolving within 6 months.

**Further investigations**

1. **Platelet count:** low, with large platelets (megathrombocytes) in the peripheral blood.
2. **Coagulation tests:** PT and PTT normal.
3. **Bone marrow:** normal or increased megakaryocytes, some immature; its importance is to rule out marrow failure or other primary marrow disorders.
4. **Exclusion of secondary causes:** systemic lupus erythematosus, HIV infection and B-cell neoplasms.
5. Tests for platelet autoantibodies have low sensitivity and specificity and are not clinically useful.


## 10-year-old boy with history of gum bleeding — Probable causes?

Excessive bleeding can result from increased fragility of vessels, platelet deficiency or dysfunction, or derangement of coagulation.

1. **Vessel wall abnormalities:** small haemorrhages, particularly of the gingivae; platelet count, PT and PTT normal. Examples are scurvy and Ehlers-Danlos syndrome [collagen defects].
2. **Thrombocytopenia:** immune thrombocytopenic purpura (gingival bleeding and easy bruising); aplastic anaemia; marrow replacement by leukaemia.
3. **Defective platelet function:** von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor]; inherited disorders of platelet function.
4. **Clotting factor deficiency:** haemophilia A and B.
5. **Acute leukaemia:** mucosal haemorrhages into the gingivae; monocytic leukaemias infiltrate the gingiva.
6. **Disseminated intravascular coagulation.**


## Boy with haemarthrosis, family history through maternal uncle (or excessive bleeding after tooth extraction / spontaneous bleeding) — Provisional diagnosis? How to confirm / which test helps?

**Provisional diagnosis:** haemophilia A (factor VIII deficiency).

**Basis**

- X-linked recessive trait, affecting mainly males; transmitted through carrier females, hence the affected maternal uncle.
- Easy bruising and massive haemorrhage after trauma or surgery.
- "Spontaneous" haemorrhages in regions susceptible to trauma, particularly the joints (haemarthroses); recurrent bleeding leads to progressive disabling deformities.
- Petechiae are characteristically absent.

**Confirmation**

1. **PTT:** prolonged.
2. **PT:** normal, pointing to an abnormality of the intrinsic pathway.
3. **Factor VIII assay:** required for diagnosis. Severity: less than 1% of normal is severe, 2% to 5% moderately severe, 6% to 50% mild.
4. Factor IX assay, to exclude haemophilia B (Christmas disease [haemophilia B, factor IX deficiency]), which is clinically indistinguishable and also shows prolonged PTT and normal PT.


## 25-year-old woman, 7 months pregnant, with anaemia — Probable causes? How will you confirm the case?

**Probable causes**

1. **Iron deficiency anaemia:** increased requirement in pregnancy; the commonest cause.
2. **Folate deficiency (megaloblastic anaemia):** increased requirement in pregnancy from increased DNA synthesis.
3. **Vitamin B12 deficiency:** relative deficiency from increased demand in pregnancy.

**Confirmation of iron deficiency**

1. Haemoglobin and haematocrit depressed, with hypochromia, microcytosis and modest poikilocytosis; pencil cells.
2. Serum iron and ferritin low.
3. Total iron-binding capacity high.
4. Transferrin saturation below 15%.
5. Absent stainable iron in marrow macrophages, on Prussian blue stain of marrow aspirate.
6. Reticulocyte rise in 5 to 7 days after oral iron.

**Confirmation of folate deficiency**

1. Macro-ovalocytes, hypersegmented neutrophils and low reticulocyte count on the blood film.
2. Decreased serum or red cell folate.
3. Raised serum homocysteine; methylmalonate normal.
4. Vitamin B12 deficiency must be excluded before folate is given.


## Ataxic gait, glossitis/weakness, anaemia/palpitation with history of partial gastrectomy — Most probable haematological diagnosis?

**Most probable diagnosis:** megaloblastic anaemia due to vitamin B12 deficiency.

**Basis**

- With gastrectomy, intrinsic factor is lost, so vitamin B12 cannot be absorbed.
- Ataxic gait: demyelination of the dorsal and lateral spinal tracts, giving sensory ataxia and paraesthesiae.
- Weakness and palpitation: symptoms of anaemia.

**Other cause after gastrectomy:** iron deficiency, because gastrectomy diminishes iron absorption, giving a microcytic hypochromic picture.

**Confirmation:** macro-ovalocytes and hypersegmented neutrophils; hypercellular marrow with megaloblasts; low serum vitamin B12; raised homocysteine and methylmalonic acid; reticulocyte rise about 5 days after parenteral vitamin B12.


## What do you mean by chronic obstructive and restrictive lung diseases?

| Feature | Obstructive lung disease | Restrictive lung disease |
|---|---|---|
| **Definition** | Increase in resistance to airflow due to diffuse airway disease, which may affect any level of the respiratory tract | Reduced expansion of lung parenchyma and decreased total lung capacity |
| **Pulmonary function tests** | Decreased maximal airflow rates during forced expiration; FEV1 over FVC ratio of less than 0.7 | Decreased total lung capacity |
| **Examples** | Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (emphysema and chronic bronchitis), asthma, bronchiectasis | Diseases that reduce lung expansion, such as the fibrosing diseases |

**Chronic obstructive** pulmonary disease is defined by the World Health Organization as a common, preventable and treatable disease characterised by persistent respiratory symptoms and airflow limitation due to airway or alveolar abnormalities caused by exposure to noxious particles or gases.

## Define emphysema.

**Emphysema** is defined by irreversible enlargement of the airspaces distal to the terminal bronchiole, accompanied by destruction of alveolar walls.

## Classify emphysema.

Classified according to the anatomic distribution within the lobule, into four major types. Only the first two cause clinically significant airflow obstruction.

| Type | Features |
|---|---|
| **Centriacinar (centrilobular)** | Most common; more than 95% of clinically significant cases; heavy smokers with COPD; central parts of the acini (respiratory bronchioles) affected, distal alveoli spared; upper lobes, particularly the apical segments |
| **Panacinar (panlobular)** | Associated with alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency and exacerbated by smoking; acini uniformly enlarged from respiratory bronchiole to terminal blind alveoli; lower zones and anterior margins of the lung, most severe at the bases |
| **Distal acinar (paraseptal)** | Distal part of the acinus involved, proximal part normal; most striking adjacent to the pleura; underlies many cases of spontaneous pneumothorax in young adults; upper half of the lungs |
| **Irregular (paracicatricial)** | Acinus irregularly involved; almost invariably associated with scarring; usually small foci and clinically insignificant |

## Write down the pathogenesis of emphysema.

Clinically significant emphysema is largely confined to smokers and to patients with alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency.

1. **Toxic injury and inflammation:** cigarette smoke and other noxious particles damage the respiratory epithelium and cause inflammation, with parenchymal destruction; mediators such as leukotriene B4, interleukin-8 and TNF are increased.
2. **Protease-antiprotease imbalance:** proteases released from inflammatory and epithelial cells break down connective tissue; there is a relative deficiency of protective antiproteases, in some cases genetic.
3. **Oxidative stress:** oxidants from tobacco smoke, alveolar damage and inflammatory cells cause tissue damage, endothelial dysfunction and inflammation.
4. **Infection:** not an initiating factor, but may acutely exacerbate existing disease.

**Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency**
- About 1% of patients with emphysema have this defect.
- Alpha-1 antitrypsin is a major inhibitor of proteases, particularly neutrophil elastase; it is encoded by the Pi locus on chromosome 14.
- More than 80% of ZZ individuals develop symptomatic panacinar emphysema, earlier and more severely if they smoke.
- Injury that increases neutrophil influx releases proteases, which in the absence of alpha-1 antitrypsin digest elastic tissue excessively.

**Airway obstruction.** Loss of elastic tissue around respiratory bronchioles reduces radial traction, so they collapse during expiration and cause functional airflow obstruction.

## Write a short note on COPD.

**Definition.** A common, preventable and treatable disease characterised by persistent respiratory symptoms and airflow limitation due to airway or alveolar abnormalities caused by exposure to noxious particles or gases (World Health Organization).

**Aetiology**
- Heavy cigarette smoking: 35% to 50% of heavy smokers develop COPD, and about 80% of COPD is attributable to smoking.
- Poor lung development early in life, environmental and occupational pollutants, airway hyperresponsiveness and certain genetic polymorphisms.

**Pathogenesis.** Emphysema and chronic bronchitis often occur together.
- Emphysema: tissue destruction by elastases and oxidants released from inflammatory cells, particularly neutrophils, responding to tobacco smoke; protease-antiprotease imbalance.
- Chronic bronchitis: mucus hypersecretion with enlargement of mucous glands and goblet cell increase; chronic inflammation with small airway fibrosis.

**Morphology**
- Emphysema: voluminous lungs, abnormally large alveoli separated by thin septa, loss of attachments to small airways, decreased capillary bed.
- Chronic bronchitis: hyperaemia, swelling and oedema of the mucosa with mucinous or mucopurulent secretions; chronic inflammation, goblet cell hyperplasia, enlarged mucous glands; Reid index [ratio of bronchial gland thickness to wall thickness] (normally 0.4) increased.

**Clinical features**
- Usually a smoking history of 40 pack-years or more; insidious dyspnoea on exertion, chronic cough with sputum.
- Spirometry: FEV1 over FVC ratio of less than 0.7.
- Pink puffers (emphysema dominant) and blue bloaters (chronic bronchitis dominant, with hypercapnia, hypoxaemia and cyanosis).

**Complications.** Pulmonary hypertension, cor pulmonale and death due to heart failure; acute respiratory failure from superimposed infection; pneumothorax from rupture of subpleural blebs.

## What is pneumonia?

**Pneumonia** can be very broadly defined as any infection of the lung parenchyma.

## Give the types of pneumonia.

Classified by the aetiologic agent or, if no pathogen can be isolated (about 50% of cases), by the clinical setting. Pneumonia arises in seven distinct clinical settings.

| Type | Common pathogens |
|---|---|
| **Community-acquired acute pneumonia** | Streptococcus pneumoniae, Haemophilus influenzae, Moraxella catarrhalis, Staphylococcus aureus, Klebsiella pneumoniae, Pseudomonas, Legionella pneumophila |
| **Community-acquired atypical pneumonia** | Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydia species, Coxiella burnetii; viruses (respiratory syncytial virus, parainfluenza virus, human metapneumovirus in children; influenza A and B in adults; adenovirus in military recruits) |
| **Hospital-acquired pneumonia** | Gram-negative rods (Klebsiella, Serratia, Escherichia coli, Pseudomonas); Staphylococcus aureus, usually methicillin-resistant |
| **Aspiration pneumonia** | Anaerobic oral flora (Bacteroides, Prevotella, Fusobacterium, Peptostreptococcus) mixed with aerobic bacteria |
| **Chronic pneumonia** | Granulomatous: Mycobacterium tuberculosis, atypical mycobacteria, Histoplasma, Coccidioides, Blastomyces |
| **Necrotising pneumonia and lung abscess** | Anaerobic bacteria, with or without mixed aerobic infection; Staphylococcus aureus, Klebsiella, Streptococcus pyogenes, type 3 pneumococcus |
| **Pneumonia in the immunocompromised host** | Opportunistic agents in addition to the usual pathogens |

By anatomic distribution, bacterial pneumonia is **lobular bronchopneumonia** or **lobar pneumonia**.

## Discuss the pathogenesis of lobar pneumonia.

- Pneumonia results when local defence mechanisms are impaired or systemic resistance is lowered.
- Local defences are compromised by loss of the cough reflex, dysfunction of the mucociliary apparatus (cigarette smoke, viral disease), accumulation of secretions, impaired alveolar macrophage activity (alcohol, tobacco smoke, anoxia) and pulmonary congestion and oedema.
- Streptococcus pneumoniae (pneumococcus) is the most common cause, and the inflammation is usually lobar.
- Often a bacterial infection follows an upper respiratory tract viral infection.
- Bacterial invasion of the lung parenchyma fills the alveoli with an inflammatory **exudate**, which causes consolidation of the pulmonary tissue.
- Predisposing conditions: extremes of age, chronic diseases (congestive heart failure, COPD, diabetes), immune deficiencies, and decreased or absent splenic function, which puts the patient at risk of infection with encapsulated bacteria such as pneumococcus.

## What are the stages of lobar pneumonia?

1. **Congestion**
2. **Red hepatisation**
3. **Grey hepatisation**
4. **Resolution**

## Discuss the morphology of lobar pneumonia.

1. **Congestion:** the lung is heavy, boggy and red; vascular engorgement, intra-alveolar oedema fluid with a few neutrophils, and numerous bacteria.
2. **Red hepatisation:** massive confluent exudation, with neutrophils, red cells and fibrin filling the alveolar spaces; the lobe is red, firm and airless, with a liver-like consistency.
3. **Grey hepatisation:** progressive disintegration of red cells with a persisting fibrinosuppurative exudate; the colour changes to greyish-brown.
4. **Resolution:** the exudate is broken down by enzymatic digestion into granular, semifluid debris, which is resorbed, ingested by macrophages, expectorated or organised by fibroblasts.

The pleural fibrinous reaction (pleuritis) may resolve, but more often is organised, leaving fibrous thickening or permanent adhesions.

## Write the complications of pneumonia.

1. Tissue destruction and necrosis, causing **abscess** formation, particularly common with pneumococcal or Klebsiella infections.
2. Spread of infection to the pleural cavity, causing an intrapleural fibrinosuppurative reaction known as **empyema**.
3. Bacteraemic dissemination to the heart valves, pericardium, brain, kidneys, spleen or joints, causing abscesses, endocarditis, meningitis or suppurative arthritis.
4. Staphylococcal pneumonia has a high incidence of complications such as lung abscess and empyema.
5. Deaths in treated patients result mostly from a complication such as empyema, meningitis, endocarditis or pericarditis, or from debility or chronic alcoholism.

## What are the differences between bronchopneumonia and lobar pneumonia?

| Feature | Bronchopneumonia | Lobar pneumonia |
|---|---|---|
| **Distribution** | Patchy consolidation of the lung | Consolidation of a large portion of a lobe or an entire lobe |
| **Gross** | Foci of acute suppurative inflammation; often multilobar, frequently bilateral and basal; slightly elevated, dry, granular, grey-red to yellow, poorly delimited at the margins | Lobe uniformly consolidated; four stages: congestion, red hepatisation, grey hepatisation and resolution |
| **Histology** | Neutrophil-rich exudate filling the bronchi, bronchioles and adjacent alveolar spaces | Alveoli filled by neutrophils, red cells and fibrin |
| **Radiology** | Focal opacities | Whole lobe radiopaque |
| **Common cause** | Not given | Streptococcus pneumoniae (the inflammation is usually lobar) |

The patterns overlap: patchy involvement may become confluent, and the same organism may produce either pattern.

## Name the etiologic agents of pneumonia.

**Bacteria**
- Streptococcus pneumoniae (pneumococcus): most common cause of community-acquired acute pneumonia.
- Haemophilus influenzae and Moraxella catarrhalis.
- Staphylococcus aureus: secondary to viral respiratory illness; also hospital-acquired.
- Klebsiella pneumoniae: chronic alcoholics and debilitated people.
- Pseudomonas aeruginosa: cystic fibrosis, neutropenia, hospital-acquired.
- Legionella pneumophila.
- Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydia species, Coxiella burnetii.
- Anaerobic oral flora: aspiration.
- Mycobacterium tuberculosis: chronic granulomatous pneumonia.

**Viruses**
- Respiratory syncytial virus, parainfluenza virus and human metapneumovirus in children; influenza A and B in adults; adenovirus in military recruits; coronavirus.

**Fungi**
- Histoplasma, Coccidioides and Blastomyces in chronic pneumonia.

## What investigations are done to diagnose a case of pneumonia?

1. **Gram-stained sputum:** numerous neutrophils containing gram-positive, lancet-shaped diplococci support pneumococcal pneumonia; false positives occur because S. pneumoniae is part of the endogenous flora in 20% of adults.
2. **Blood culture:** more specific but less sensitive; only 20% to 30% are positive in the early phase of illness.
3. **Culture and other assays:** often necessary to establish the diagnosis, since clinical and radiologic features do not clearly distinguish viral from bacterial infection.
4. **Legionella:** polymerase chain reaction on sputum or Legionella antigen in the urine; culture is the gold standard but takes 3 to 5 days.
5. **Chest imaging:** the whole lobe is radiopaque in lobar pneumonia and there are focal opacities in bronchopneumonia.
6. Identification of the organism and its antibiotic sensitivity are the keystones of therapy.

## Write a short note on lung abscess.

**Definition.** A local suppurative process that produces necrosis of lung tissue.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**
- Any bacterial pathogen can produce an abscess; most commonly aerobic and anaerobic streptococci, S. aureus and gram-negative organisms. Anaerobic oral organisms (Bacteroides, Fusobacterium, Peptococcus) are the exclusive isolates in about 60% of cases.
1. **Aspiration of infective material** (the most frequent cause): suppressed cough reflex (alcohol, opioids, coma, anaesthesia, seizures), severe dysphagia, protracted vomiting, poor dental hygiene.
2. **Antecedent primary lung infection:** postpneumonic abscess, usually with S. aureus, K. pneumoniae and pneumococcus.
3. **Septic embolism:** from thrombophlebitis or right-sided infective endocarditis.
4. **Neoplasia:** postobstructive pneumonia.
5. Miscellaneous: trauma, direct extension from adjacent suppurative infection, haematogenous seeding; some are primary cryptogenic.

**Morphology**
- From a few millimetres to cavities of 5 to 6 cm; single or multiple.
- Aspiration abscesses are more common on the right and usually single; those from pneumonia or bronchiectasis are multiple and basal; septic emboli give multiple abscesses.
- Suppurative destruction of lung parenchyma around a central cavity; a fibrous wall in chronic cases.
- Continued infection gives fetid, green-black, multilocular cavities called gangrene of the lung.

**Clinical features**
- Cough, fever, and copious foul-smelling purulent or sanguineous sputum; chest pain, weight loss; clubbing.
- Confirmed radiologically; in older individuals rule out underlying carcinoma, present in 10% to 15%.

**Complications.** Extension into the pleural cavity, haemorrhage, brain abscess or meningitis from septic emboli, and rarely secondary (AA) amyloidosis.

## What are Ghon's focus and Ghon's complex?

- **Ghon focus [primary subpleural tuberculous lesion]:** a 1 to 1.5 cm area of grey-white inflammation with consolidation, in the lower part of the upper lobe or upper part of the lower lobe close to the pleura, in primary tuberculosis; its centre usually undergoes caseous necrosis.
- **Ghon complex:** the combination of the parenchymal lung lesion (Ghon focus) and caseating involvement of the regional lymph nodes.
- In about 95% of cases cell-mediated immunity controls the infection, and the Ghon complex undergoes progressive fibrosis, often followed by radiologically detectable calcification.

## What are the sites of primary and secondary tuberculosis in lung?

| Type | Site |
|---|---|
| **Primary tuberculosis** | Distal airspaces of the lower part of the upper lobe or the upper part of the lower lobe, usually close to the pleura |
| **Secondary tuberculosis** | Apex of the upper lobes of one or both lungs; the initial lesion is within 1 to 2 cm of the apical pleura |

## What are the fates of primary tuberculosis of lung?

1. **Healing:** in about 95% of cases cell-mediated immunity controls the infection; the Ghon complex [primary subpleural tuberculous lesion] undergoes fibrosis and calcification, and no lesions develop despite seeding of other organs.
2. **Dormancy:** viable organisms may remain dormant in the lesion for decades and be reactivated if immune defences are lowered.
3. **Progressive primary tuberculosis:** clinically significant disease develops in about 5% of newly infected people; it resembles acute bacterial pneumonia, with consolidation of the lobe, hilar lymphadenopathy and pleural effusion.
4. **Dissemination:** lymphatic and haematogenous spread may cause tuberculous meningitis and miliary tuberculosis.

## Give in short the pathogenesis of pulmonary tuberculosis.

1. **Entry into macrophages:** M. tuberculosis is phagocytosed by alveolar macrophages through receptors such as mannose-binding lectin and complement receptor 3.
2. **Replication in macrophages:** the bacterium blocks phagosome-lysosome fusion by recruiting coronin to the phagosome membrane, so it replicates unchecked; in the first 3 weeks bacteraemia seeds multiple sites.
3. **Innate immunity:** lipoarabinomannan binds TLR2 and unmethylated CpG nucleotides bind TLR9.
4. **Th1 response:** about 3 weeks after infection, dendritic cells carry antigen to lymph nodes; interleukin-12 and interleukin-18 drive differentiation of Th1 cells.
5. **Macrophage activation:** Th1 cells produce interferon-gamma, which stimulates nitric oxide and reactive oxygen species, antimicrobial peptides and autophagy to kill the bacteria.
6. **Granuloma and caseation:** activated macrophages become epithelioid histiocytes that form granulomas, with giant cells; if infection progresses, the immune response causes caseous necrosis. TNF recruits more monocytes.
7. This response is largely effective, but causes tissue destruction; loss of T cell immunity is shown by tuberculin negativity in a previously positive person.

## Define miliary tuberculosis.

Miliary pulmonary tuberculosis occurs when organisms draining through lymphatics enter the venous blood and circulate back to the lung; the lesions are microscopic or small visible foci (**2 mm**) of yellow-white consolidation, resembling millet seeds. Systemic **miliary tuberculosis** occurs when bacteria disseminate through the systemic arterial system.

## Discuss the pathological characteristics of secondary pulmonary TB.

**Gross**
- The initial lesion is a small focus of consolidation, less than 2 cm, within 1 to 2 cm of the apical pleura.
- The focus is sharply circumscribed, firm, grey-white to yellow, with central caseation and peripheral fibrosis.
- In immunocompetent people it becomes fibrously encapsulated, leaving fibrocalcific scars.
- Progressive disease: the apical lesion expands, erodes into bronchi and vessels, and evacuates the caseous centre, leaving a ragged, irregular cavity poorly walled off by fibrous tissue; erosion of vessels causes haemoptysis.
- Cavity erosion into an airway is an important source of spread.

**Microscopy**
- Coalescent tubercles with central caseation, epithelioid cells and multinucleated giant cells, within a fibroblastic rim with lymphocytes.
- Acid-fast bacilli are found in early exudative and caseous phases, but are usually too few in late fibrocalcific stages.

**Differences from primary disease**
- Because of preexisting hypersensitivity, the bacilli elicit a prompt tissue response that walls off the focus, so the regional lymph nodes are less prominently involved early.
- Cavitation occurs readily if untreated.

**Spread.** Along airways, lymphatics and vessels, giving miliary disease, pleural effusion, tuberculous empyema, obliterative fibrous pleuritis, and endobronchial, endotracheal and laryngeal tuberculosis.

## Write a short note on Mantoux test/tuberculin test.

**Principle.** The tuberculin (purified protein derivative, or Mantoux [tuberculin skin test]) test is performed by intracutaneous injection of purified protein derivative of M. tuberculosis, which induces a visible and palpable induration that peaks in 48 to 72 hours.

**Interpretation**
- A positive test signifies T cell mediated immunity to mycobacterial antigens, but does not differentiate between infection (current or past) and active disease.
- False negative: certain viral infections, sarcoidosis, malnutrition, Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells], immunosuppression and active tuberculous disease.
- False positive: infection by atypical mycobacteria, or prior BCG vaccination.

Interferon-gamma release assays are an in vitro alternative, with uncommon false positives.

## A patient has cough, evening rise of temperature, weight loss (± cervical lymphadenopathy, apical opacity). What is your probable diagnosis?

**Pulmonary tuberculosis**, most likely secondary (postprimary) tuberculosis.

- Systemic symptoms: malaise, anorexia, weight loss and low grade fever that appears late each afternoon and then subsides, with night sweats.
