- Cough with sputum; haemoptysis in about one-half of cases.
- Secondary pulmonary tuberculosis classically involves the apex of the upper lobes.
- Cervical lymphadenopathy suggests tuberculous lymphadenitis (scrofula), the most frequent presentation of extrapulmonary tuberculosis.

## Same: what laboratory investigations/tests (incl. on lymph node) confirm it?

1. **Acid-fast smears and cultures of sputum:** culture on solid media shows growth at 3 to 6 weeks, in liquid media within 2 weeks; culture is the gold standard and allows susceptibility testing.
2. **Polymerase chain reaction:** rapid diagnosis, and detects rifampin resistance; as sensitive as culture in smear-positive samples.
3. **Tuberculin skin test or interferon-gamma release assay:** shows T cell immunity, but does not distinguish infection from active disease.
4. **Lymph node biopsy:** granulomas with caseation, enclosed within a fibroblastic rim, with multinucleated giant cells; acid-fast stain is indicated whenever granulomas are present, even without caseous necrosis.

## Same (27y with hemoptysis): what microscopic findings do you expect?

- Characteristic granulomatous inflammation with **caseating** and noncaseating **tubercles**.
- Central granular caseation, surrounded by epithelioid cells and multinucleate giant cells.
- Granulomas usually enclosed within a fibroblastic rim punctuated by lymphocytes.
- Acid-fast bacilli can often be found in early exudative and caseous phases, but are usually too few in late fibrocalcific stages.
- Immunocompromised people do not form the characteristic granulomas, and their macrophages contain many bacilli.

## Classify tumours of lung (WHO classification of bronchogenic carcinoma).

| Category | Proportion |
|---|---|
| **Adenocarcinoma** (includes adenocarcinoma in situ, minimally invasive adenocarcinoma, and invasive types: lepidic, acinar, papillary, micropapillary, solid, mucinous, colloid, enteric-type, foetal) | 50% |
| **Squamous cell carcinoma** | 20% |
| **Small cell carcinoma** | 15% |
| **Large cell carcinoma** | 2% |
| **Other** (pleomorphic carcinoma, carcinosarcoma, pulmonary blastoma; salivary gland-type tumours such as adenoid cystic carcinoma, epithelial-myoepithelial carcinoma and mucoepidermoid carcinoma) | 13% |

Lung cancers are broadly classified into small cell and non-small cell types. Combinations of squamous cell carcinoma and adenocarcinoma, or small cell and squamous cell carcinoma, occur in about 14% and 5% of patients.

## Write down briefly the morphology of small cell carcinoma of lung.

- Relatively small cells with scant cytoplasm and ill-defined cell borders.
- Finely granular nuclear chromatin (**salt and pepper** pattern), with absent or inconspicuous nucleoli.
- Cells are round, oval or spindle-shaped, with prominent **nuclear moulding**.
- Smaller than three times the diameter of a small resting lymphocyte (about 25 µm).
- Mitotic count is invariably high.
- Necrosis is common and often extensive.
- Basophilic staining of vascular walls from encrustation by DNA of necrotic tumour cells (**Azzopardi effect** [DNA deposition on vessel walls in necrotic small cell carcinoma]).
- Electron microscopy: dense-core neurosecretory granules, about 100 nm.

## How will you diagnose/investigate a case of bronchogenic carcinoma?

1. **Sputum cytology:** keratinised squamous carcinoma cells with prominent hyperchromatic nuclei.
2. **Biopsy:** including biopsy of tissues involved by metastatic disease.
3. **Histology with immunohistochemistry:** TTF-1 in adenocarcinoma; p40 for squamous differentiation in poorly differentiated tumours.
4. **Mutation testing in adenocarcinoma:** EGFR (about 15% of all cases) and KRAS (about 30% of adenocarcinomas).
5. **Staging by TNM system:** by tumour size and involvement of thoracic structures.
6. **Early detection:** low-dose computed tomography in high-risk individuals.

## A smoker has cough, hemoptysis, chest mass/opacity, supraclavicular or cervical node. What is the most likely diagnosis?

**Bronchogenic carcinoma (lung cancer).**

- Cough, haemoptysis and weight loss are common presenting features; cough is the commonest (75%).
- About 80% of lung cancers occur in active smokers.
- Supraclavicular nodal involvement is N3 disease, and metastases to bronchial, tracheal and mediastinal nodes are found in most cases.
- Confirm by sputum cytology and biopsy.

## What is Barrett esophagus?

Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus] is a complication of chronic GERD that is characterised by **intestinal metaplasia of the oesophageal squamous mucosa** and is associated with an **increased risk of cancer**.

## Mention the clinical importance of Barrett esophagus.

- Greatest concern: increased risk of **oesophageal adenocarcinoma**; most oesophageal adenocarcinomas occur with Barrett oesophagus [intestinal metaplasia of the lower oesophagus], but most individuals with Barrett oesophagus do not develop tumours
- Risk of cancer is greatest in those with **goblet cells**
- **Dysplasia** (low grade or high grade) is a preinvasive neoplastic change, associated with prolonged symptoms, longer segment length, increased age and European ethnicity
- **Long-segment** (3 cm or more) disease carries higher risk of dysplasia or carcinoma than **short-segment** (less than 3 cm)
- Identified by **endoscopy and biopsy**; surveillance biopsy protocol supported by many experts
- Low-grade dysplasia: conservative management with more frequent surveillance, or radiofrequency ablation or endoscopic mucosal resection
- Multifocal high-grade dysplasia or intramucosal carcinoma: ablation, endoscopic mucosal resection or oesophagectomy

## What are the ulcerative lesions/different ulcers of GIT?

- **Peptic ulcer**: chronic ulcer of the duodenum or stomach
- **Stress-related ulcers** (acute): in shock, sepsis, severe trauma, extensive burns, intracranial disease or major surgery
  - **Curling ulcers**: proximal duodenum and stomach in severe burns or trauma
  - **Cushing ulcers**: stomach, duodenum and oesophagus in intracranial disease; greater risk of perforation
- **Viral oesophageal ulcers**: discrete punched-out ulcers, for example herpes simplex virus
- Ulcers of **Crohn disease** [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease] and **ulcerative colitis**

## What is peptic ulcer disease (PUD)?

Peptic ulcer disease (PUD) refers to **chronic mucosal ulceration** affecting the **duodenum or stomach** and is almost always associated with ***H. pylori* infection, NSAIDs, or cigarette smoking**.

## Mention the common site of PUD.

- **Proximal duodenum**, within a few centimetres of the pyloric valve: the most frequent site
- **Stomach**: predominantly along the **lesser curvature**, near the interface of the body and antrum
- Also the gastric antrum

## Write down the predisposing factors for peptic ulcer.

- ***Helicobacter pylori* infection**
- **Cigarette use** (synergises with H. pylori for gastric PUD)
- **NSAIDs**, including aspirin (potentiated by corticosteroids)
- **Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease**
- **Alcoholic cirrhosis** (primarily duodenal PUD)
- **Illicit drugs**, for example cocaine, that reduce mucosal blood flow
- **Cardiovascular disease** (reduced mucosal blood flow, oxygenation and healing)
- **Psychological stress** (can increase gastric acid secretion)
- **Endocrine cell hyperplasia** (can stimulate parietal cell growth and gastric acid secretion)
- **Zollinger-Ellison syndrome** [gastrin-secreting tumour causing severe peptic ulceration] (PUD of stomach, duodenum and jejunum)
- **Viral infection** (cytomegalovirus, herpes simplex virus)

## Write in short about the pathogenesis of PUD.

- PUD results from **imbalance between defence mechanisms and damaging factors** that cause chronic gastritis; it generally develops on a background of chronic gastritis
- Defences: mucus and phospholipid layer, bicarbonate secretion, epithelial barrier, mucosal blood flow
- Damaging factors: gastric acid, peptic enzymes, H. pylori, NSAIDs
- ***H. pylori* antral gastritis** leads to increased local gastrin production, expansion of gastric body parietal cells and **increased acid secretion**, with **decreased duodenal bicarbonate secretion**
- **NSAIDs** inhibit COX-dependent synthesis of prostaglandins E2 and I2, which support mucus, bicarbonate and phospholipid secretion, mucosal blood flow and epithelial restitution
- Individuals with gastric mucosal atrophy (reduced acid secretion) are generally protected from antral and duodenal ulcers
- Why only some people develop PUD is poorly understood: host factors and variation in bacterial pathogenicity are involved

## Explain: H. pylori does not invade the tissues but can cause peptic ulcer.

- H. pylori has adapted to the niche provided by **gastric mucus**; it stays in the superficial mucus over surface and neck epithelial cells and does not invade the tissue
- It causes injury through its virulence factors:
  - **Flagella**: motility in viscous mucus
  - **Urease**: generates ammonia from endogenous urea and raises local gastric pH
  - **Adhesins**: adherence to surface foveolar cells
  - **Toxins**, such as **CagA** (injected into the epithelial cytoplasm) and **VacA**
- It induces **antral gastritis** with infiltration by neutrophils and plasma cells; inflammation enhances local gastrin production, leading to increased acid secretion and decreased duodenal bicarbonate secretion
- Increased acid with impaired mucosal defence leads to **peptic ulcer**
- The outcome depends on host defences, host genetics and immune response, and bacterial virulence

## How will you differentiate a benign gastric ulcer from a malignant ulcer?

| Feature | Benign (peptic) ulcer | Malignant ulcer (gastric adenocarcinoma) |
|---|---|---|
| Shape | Round to oval, sharply **punched-out** defect | Ulcerated infiltrative tumour |
| Margin | Level with surrounding mucosa; may overhang the base on the proximal side | **Heaped-up** margins |
| Base | **Smooth and clean** because of peptic digestion of exudate | Tumour tissue |
| Number | Solitary in more than 80% | Not stated |
| Site | Lesser curvature near the interface of body and antrum | Most often the antrum; lesser curvature more than greater |
| Surrounding mucosa | Scarring draws mucosa into folds that radiate outward | Desmoplastic reaction, rigid thickened wall |
| Microscopy | Fibrinoid debris, granulation tissue and fibrous scar | Neoplastic glands (intestinal type) or **signet-ring cells** (diffuse type) |

## Write the microscopic features/morphology of peptic ulcer.

**Gross**
- Solitary in more than 80%; round to oval, sharply **punched-out** defect
- Margin usually level with the surrounding mucosa; may overhang the base proximally
- Base **smooth and clean** because of peptic digestion of exudate
- Scarring may involve the entire thickness of the wall and draw surrounding mucosa into radiating folds

**Microscopy** (base of an active ulcer, from surface to depth)
1. Thin layer of **fibrinoid debris** with a predominantly **neutrophilic** infiltrate
2. **Granulation tissue** with immature vessels, neutrophils and mononuclear leucocytes
3. **Fibrous or collagenous scar**
- Larger vessels in the scarred area are thickened and occasionally thrombosed
- Foveolar metaplasia is common in chronic duodenal peptic disease

## Mention the complications of PUD.

1. **Haemorrhage**: bleeding from thickened vessels in the scarred base may be life-threatening; **iron deficiency anaemia** may result
2. **Perforation** into the peritoneal cavity: surgical emergency, free air under the diaphragm on upright abdominal radiograph; accounts for two-thirds of ulcer-related deaths; rarely the first indication of an ulcer
3. **Obstruction**: most often with pyloric channel ulcers (may occur with duodenal ulcers); incapacitating crampy abdominal pain; rarely total obstruction with intractable vomiting
- **Penetrating ulcers**: pain referred to the back, left upper quadrant or chest

## A patient with long-standing PUD has repeated/prolonged vomiting. What will be his electrolyte and acid-base status?

- **Hypochloraemic, hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis**
- Persistent vomiting from gastric outlet obstruction (ulcers of the pyloric channel and first part of the duodenum) causes loss of gastric acid, leading to metabolic alkalosis
- **Dehydration** accompanies continuous vomiting
- The alkalosis responds to administration of chloride

## Same (40y): what test confirms it in the laboratory?

- **Serum electrolytes**: low chloride (hypochloraemia), low potassium (hypokalaemia), raised bicarbonate
- Arterial or venous blood gas analysis: metabolic alkalosis
- Cause of the vomiting: **upper endoscopy** (visualises the gastric outlet) and barium upper GI study (shows the site of obstruction)

## Same (55y): what is the likely diagnosis of his present illness?

- **Gastric outlet obstruction (acquired pyloric stenosis) due to chronic peptic ulcer** near the pylorus, with scarring
- Peptic ulcer obstruction is most often associated with pyloric channel ulcers; it causes crampy abdominal pain and, rarely, total obstruction with intractable vomiting
- Acquired pyloric stenosis in adults is also caused by antral gastritis, and by **carcinomas of the distal stomach and pancreas** that narrow the pyloric channel by fibrosis or malignant infiltration

## A 30y man has epigastric pain after meals and at late night with nausea, vomiting. What is the likely diagnosis?

- **Peptic ulcer disease** (duodenal ulcer)
- Epigastric burning or aching pain, 1 to 3 hours after meals in the day, worse at night (usually between 11 p.m. and 2 a.m.), relieved by alkali or food
- Nausea, vomiting, bloating, belching and weight loss
- Most commonly due to chronic H. pylori-induced antral gastritis; the proximal duodenum is the most frequent site

## Same: what complications can occur?

1. **Haemorrhage** and **iron deficiency anaemia**
2. **Perforation** into the peritoneal cavity
3. **Obstruction** (pyloric channel ulcers): crampy pain, intractable vomiting
- Penetrating ulcers: pain referred to the back, left upper quadrant or chest

## Write a short note on GIST.

**Definition:** Gastrointestinal stromal tumour (GIST) is the most common mesenchymal tumour of the abdomen; more than half occur in the stomach. It arises from the **interstitial cells of Cajal** [pacemaker cells of the gut] (pacemaker cells). Peak age is 60 years.

**Pathogenesis**
- About 75% have gain-of-function mutations in the receptor tyrosine kinase **KIT**
- About 8% have mutations in **PDGFRA**; KIT and PDGFRA mutations are mutually exclusive
- Tumours without these often have mutations in the succinate dehydrogenase complex genes (SDHA, SDHB, SDHC, SDHD)
- Associated with **Carney triad** [gastric GIST, paraganglioma and pulmonary chondroma] (GIST, paraganglioma, pulmonary chondroma) and **neurofibromatosis type 1**

**Morphology**
- Solitary, well-circumscribed, fleshy mass, up to 30 cm; overlying mucosa frequently ulcerated; cut surface **whorled**
- **Spindle cell type**: bundles of spindle cells; **epithelioid type**: plump epithelial-looking cells; mixtures are common; nuclear pleomorphism uncommon
- **KIT (CD117)** positive in 95% of gastric GISTs; **DOG1** positive in 95%

**Clinical features and complications**
- Mass effects; mucosal ulceration causes blood loss, and about half present with **anaemia**
- Metastasis to serosal nodules in the peritoneal cavity and to the liver; spread outside the abdomen is uncommon
- Prognosis correlates with size, mitotic index and location; gastric GISTs are less aggressive than small intestinal GISTs
- Treatment: complete surgical resection; **imatinib** (tyrosine kinase inhibitor) in KIT or PDGFRA mutated tumours that are unresectable, recurrent or metastatic

## List four congenital anomalies of GIT.

1. **Atresia**, fistulae and duplications (oesophageal atresia with **tracheo-oesophageal fistula**)
2. **Meckel diverticulum**
3. **Congenital hypertrophic pyloric stenosis**
4. **Hirschsprung disease** [congenital aganglionic megacolon] (congenital aganglionic megacolon)

## Write a short note on Hirschsprung disease.

**Definition:** Hirschsprung disease [congenital aganglionic megacolon] causes **functional obstruction of the colon** due to failure of ganglion cells to migrate to the wall of the colon. Incidence about 1 in 5000 live births; 10% of cases occur in **Down syndrome** [trisomy 21].

**Pathogenesis**
- Arrested migration of neural crest cells from caecum to rectum, or premature death of ganglion cells
- Distal segment lacks the **Meissner submucosal plexus [enteric nerve plexus of the submucosa] and Auerbach myenteric plexus** [enteric nerve plexus between the muscle layers] (aganglionosis); peristalsis is absent, the aganglionic segment is hypercontracted and the proximal segment dilates
- Heterozygous loss-of-function mutations of **RET** account for most familial and about 15% of sporadic cases; also **EDNRB** and **EDN3**
- Incomplete penetrance

**Morphology**
- Diagnosis needs histological absence of ganglion cells in the affected segment (acetylcholinesterase staining helps)
- Rectum always affected; **short-segment** (rectosigmoid, more common) or **long-segment**
- Proximal normally innervated colon dilates progressively (**megacolon**); may rupture near the caecum

**Clinical features and complications**
- Failure to pass meconium, constipation, abdominal distension, bilious vomiting
- Life-threatening: **enterocolitis**, fluid and electrolyte disturbances, perforation, peritonitis
- Treatment: surgical resection of the aganglionic segment

## List five causes of malabsorption syndrome.

1. **Pancreatic insufficiency** (chronic pancreatitis, **cystic fibrosis**)
2. **Coeliac disease** (gluten-sensitive enteropathy)
3. **Crohn disease** [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease]
4. Intestinal **graft-versus-host disease** after allogeneic haematopoietic stem cell transplantation
5. **Whipple disease** [Tropheryma whipplei infection causing malabsorption]

## What is inflammatory bowel disease?

Inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) is a **chronic condition comprised of two disorders, ulcerative colitis and Crohn disease** [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease].

## Mention three important features of Crohn disease.

1. **Skip lesions**: multiple, separate, sharply delineated areas of disease; most commonly the terminal ileum, ileocaecal valve and caecum
2. **Transmural inflammation** with noncaseating granulomas (about 35% of cases) and a thickened, rubbery wall
3. **Strictures, fissures and fistulae**; aphthous and linear ulcers give a cobblestone mucosa; creeping fat

## Name the inflammatory bowel diseases.

- **Crohn disease** [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease]
- **Ulcerative colitis**
- **Indeterminate colitis**: in up to 10% of IBD patients it is not possible to establish either diagnosis

## Give the differences between Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis.

| Feature | Crohn disease [transmural granulomatous inflammatory bowel disease] | Ulcerative colitis |
|---|---|---|
| Distribution | Any area of the GI tract; most commonly terminal ileum, ileocaecal valve and caecum | Colon and rectum only; **always involves the rectum** |
| Pattern | **Skip lesions** | **Continuous**, no skip lesions |
| Depth | **Transmural** | Limited to mucosa and superficial submucosa |
| Ulcers | Aphthous, then elongated serpentine ulcers; **cobblestone** mucosa | Ulcers along the long axis, not serpentine; **pseudopolyps** and mucosal bridges |
| Wall | Thickened and rubbery; **creeping fat**; strictures common | Not thickened; serosa normal; strictures do not occur |
| Fissures and fistulae | Present | Absent |
| Granulomas | **Noncaseating granulomas** in about 35% | Absent |
| Perforation | Common | Uncommon; only in toxic megacolon |
| Small intestine | Involved (about 40% limited to small intestine) | Not affected; mild **backwash ileitis** in severe pancolitis |
| Antibodies | Anti-Saccharomyces cerevisiae antibodies | **p-ANCA** in up to 75% (under 10% in Crohn disease) |
| Smoking | Strong risk factor | Onset may follow smoking cessation |
| Primary sclerosing cholangitis | Association much weaker | Association much stronger |

## Write a short note on villous adenoma.

**Definition:** Colorectal **adenomas** are epithelial neoplasms characterised by **epithelial dysplasia**; they are classified as tubular, tubulovillous or **villous** by architecture. Villous adenomas are covered by slender villi.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**
- Adenomas are precursors to **colorectal adenocarcinoma**; most adenomas do not progress to cancer
- Developed in about 30% of adults in the Western world by 60 years

**Morphology**
- 0.3 to 10 cm, pedunculated or sessile; surface velvety or raspberry-like
- Dysplasia: nuclear **hyperchromasia, elongation and stratification**, prominent nucleoli, eosinophilic cytoplasm, fewer goblet cells

**Clinical features and complications**
- Most are silent; large polyps cause **occult bleeding** and anaemia
- Villous adenomas harbour cancer more often than tubular adenomas, which also correlates with larger size
- **Size** is the most important predictor of malignancy: rare below 1 cm, nearly 40% of lesions larger than 4 cm contain invasive cancer
- High-grade dysplasia is a risk factor for cancer in that polyp
- Invasion beyond the muscularis mucosae, including the stalk, is invasive adenocarcinoma with risk of spread
- Surveillance colonoscopy from 45 years; polypectomy is diagnostic and therapeutic

## Define polyp.

Most, if not all, polyps begin as small elevations of the mucosa. These are referred to as **sessile**; as they enlarge a stalk may form and they are termed **pedunculated**.

## Write down the types/names of GI polyps.

| Group | Types |
|---|---|
| **Non-neoplastic** | **Inflammatory** polyps; hyperplastic polyps; hamartomatous polyps (juvenile polyp, Peutz-Jeghers polyp [hamartomatous gastrointestinal polyps with mucocutaneous pigmentation]) |
| **Neoplastic** | **Adenoma** (tubular, tubulovillous, villous); sessile serrated polyp |
| **Stomach** | Inflammatory or hyperplastic polyps; fundic gland polyps; gastric adenoma |

## Mention the polyps having malignant potential.

- **Adenomas**: tubular, tubulovillous and villous (precursors to colorectal adenocarcinoma)
- **Sessile serrated polyps** (right colon)
- **Gastric adenomas**: risk correlates with size, particularly above 2 cm
- **Juvenile polyposis syndrome**: 30% to 50% develop colonic adenocarcinoma by 45 years
- **Peutz-Jeghers syndrome** [hamartomatous gastrointestinal polyps with mucocutaneous pigmentation] polyps: lifetime risk of malignancy about 40%
- **Familial adenomatous polyposis**: adenomas and dysplasia in fundic gland polyps
- Hyperplastic polyps have **no** malignant potential

## Write a short note on carcinoid tumour.

**Definition:** Carcinoid tumour is a **neuroendocrine tumour** of the gastrointestinal tract. Peak incidence is the sixth decade.

**Morphology**
- Intramural or submucosal masses forming polypoid lesions; yellow or tan, very firm because of intense desmoplasia, which may kink and obstruct the small bowel
- Islands, trabeculae, strands, glands and sheets of uniform cells with scant pink granular cytoplasm and **salt and pepper** chromatin
- Positive for **synaptophysin** and **chromogranin A**

**Clinical features**
- Symptoms reflect hormones released: gastrin causes **Zollinger-Ellison syndrome** [gastrin-secreting tumour causing severe peptic ulceration]
- **Carcinoid syndrome** in fewer than 10%: cutaneous flushing, sweating, bronchospasm, colicky abdominal pain, diarrhoea, right-sided cardiac valvular fibrosis
- Syndrome is strongly associated with metastasis to the liver, as the liver inactivates the substances on first pass

**Behaviour**
- Prognosis depends on differentiation, mitotic rate and Ki-67 index
- Stomach and duodenum: rarely metastasise
- Jejunum and ileum: often multiple and aggressive
- Appendix: at the tip, rarely more than 2 cm, almost always benign
- Rectum: small, metastasis uncommon

## A young male has acute abdominal pain, vomiting, fever, RIF tenderness, neutrophilic leukocytosis. What is your diagnosis?

- **Acute appendicitis**
- Periumbilical pain that localises to the right lower quadrant, then nausea, vomiting, low-grade fever
- Deep tenderness at **McBurney point** (two-thirds of the distance from the umbilicus to the right anterior superior iliac spine)
- Neutrophilic leucocytosis
- Most common in adolescents and young adults; initiated by increased intraluminal pressure that compromises venous outflow, usually from a **faecalith**

## Same: write down the possible complications.

- **Perforation** and rupture with **suppurative peritonitis**
- Focal abscesses in the wall (acute suppurative appendicitis); **gangrenous appendicitis**
- **Pylephlebitis**
- **Portal venous thrombosis**
- **Liver abscess**
- **Bacteraemia**

## Same: what do you expect in the histopathology after operation?

- **Neutrophilic infiltration of the muscularis propria**: required for the diagnosis
- Early: congested subserosal vessels, modest perivascular neutrophilic infiltrate in all layers; mucosal neutrophils and focal superficial ulceration are not specific
- More severe: neutrophilic exudate with **serosal fibrinopurulent reaction**
- **Acute suppurative appendicitis**: focal abscesses within the wall
- **Acute gangrenous appendicitis**: ischaemia, haemorrhagic ulceration and gangrenous necrosis extending to the serosa, followed by rupture and suppurative peritonitis

## Write a short note on occult blood test.

**Definition:** Test for hidden (occult) blood in the stool, blood not visible to the eye.

**Principle**
- Detects haemoglobin or its breakdown products; the most common method detects haem
- **Guaiac test**: the pseudoperoxidase of haemoglobin converts guaiac to blue
- **Faecal immunochemical test (FIT)**: uses antibodies to detect blood

**Indications**
- Anaemia, suspected gastrointestinal bleeding, **colon cancer screening**

**Causes of a positive test**
- Peptic ulcer, carcinoma, diverticulosis, large adenomas, gastric antral vascular ectasia

**Precautions**
- Any source of blood gives a positive guaiac test, including bleeding haemorrhoids and menstruation
- Avoid red meat and vitamin C before a guaiac test
- Normal faecal blood loss can approach 1.5 mL per day

## Name common pathological conditions of hepatomegaly.

- Hepatitis A, B and C
- **Alcohol use**
- **Steatosis** (fatty liver)
- **Cancer metastases** to the liver; hepatocellular carcinoma
- **Congestive heart failure**
- **Leukaemia**
- **Primary biliary cholangitis** and sclerosing cholangitis
- **Portal vein thrombosis**
- **Sarcoidosis**
- **Glycogen storage disease**
- **Infectious mononucleosis**
- **Niemann-Pick disease** [sphingomyelinase deficiency, a lysosomal storage disease]; Reye syndrome [acute encephalopathy with fatty liver after aspirin in children]; hereditary fructose intolerance

## Name the hepatotropic viruses.

- **Hepatitis A virus** (single-stranded RNA)
- **Hepatitis B virus** (partially double-stranded DNA)
- **Hepatitis C virus** (single-stranded RNA)
- **Hepatitis D virus** (circular defective single-stranded RNA; needs hepatitis B for replication)
- **Hepatitis E virus** (single-stranded RNA)

## Discuss the clinical outcomes of hepatitis B virus infection.

1. **Acute infection with recovery**: mild or subclinical in nearly two-thirds of adults; in most cases self-limited and resolves without treatment; anti-HBs appears and persists for life
2. **Acute symptomatic hepatitis**: anorexia, fever, jaundice, upper right quadrant pain; immune complex phenomena such as **glomerulonephritis** and **polyarteritis nodosa**
3. **Acute liver failure**: rare, about 0.1% to 0.5% of acutely infected, in 4 to 8 weeks
4. **Chronic infection**: 5% to 10% of infected individuals; risk is inversely related to age and about 90% in infants infected at birth
   - Chronic hepatitis, then **cirrhosis**
   - **Hepatocellular carcinoma**; greatest risk in those infected at birth
5. **Carrier state**: "healthy carrier" has HBsAg and anti-HBe without HBeAg, normal aminotransferases, low or undetectable HBV DNA and no significant liver injury

## Mention the microscopic features of liver in acute and chronic viral hepatitis.

| | Acute viral hepatitis | Chronic viral hepatitis |
|---|---|---|
| Inflammation | Portal and **lobular** infiltrate, predominantly lymphocytes, with plasma cells and eosinophils | Portal **lymphocytic or lymphoplasmacytic** inflammation: the defining feature |
| Hepatocyte injury | Necrosis or apoptosis (**acidophil bodies**); pigmented macrophages scavenge debris | **Interface activity**: inflammatory cells cross the limiting plate and injure periportal hepatocytes |
| Severe cases | **Confluent necrosis**, panlobular necrosis, **bridging necrosis**; massive necrosis may cause liver failure | **Fibrosis**: portal and periportal, then portoportal bridging, then cirrhosis |
| Specific features | None | Hepatitis B: **ground-glass hepatocytes** (endoplasmic reticulum filled with HBsAg). Hepatitis C: **portal lymphoid aggregates or follicles**, steatosis, focal bile duct injury |

## What alterations occur in liver function tests in acute viral hepatitis / chronic liver disease?

**Acute viral hepatitis**
- Serum **transaminases** markedly elevated
- **Jaundice** with raised bilirubin
- In liver failure, falling transaminases with worsening jaundice and coagulopathy mean few viable hepatocytes remain

**Chronic hepatitis and chronic liver disease**
- Persistently elevated **transaminases**, sometimes the only evidence of chronicity
- **Prolonged prothrombin time**
- **Hyperglobulinaemia**
- **Hyperbilirubinaemia**
- Mild elevation of **alkaline phosphatase**
- Decreased serum **albumin** in impaired synthetic function; increased ammonia

## Define cirrhosis of liver.

Cirrhosis is a condition marked by **diffuse remodelling of the liver into parenchymal nodules** (often regenerative) **surrounded by fibrous bands** and a variable degree of **vascular (often portosystemic) shunting**.

## Name the etiology of cirrhosis.

- **Chronic hepatitis B**
- **Chronic hepatitis C**
- **Metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease**
- **Alcohol-associated liver disease**
- Metabolic diseases
- **Cryptogenic cirrhosis**: cirrhosis arising without any clear cause

## Write the etiopathogenesis of cirrhosis.

**Aetiology:** chronic hepatitis B, chronic hepatitis C, metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease, alcohol-associated liver disease, metabolic diseases; some cases are cryptogenic.

**Pathogenesis**
1. Persistent injury and inflammation activate the **hepatic stellate cell**, the principal cell of scar deposition; when quiescent it stores lipid, including vitamin A
2. Stimuli for activation: inflammatory cytokines such as **TNF-alpha** from Kupffer cells [macrophages lining the hepatic sinusoids] and macrophages; altered interaction with extracellular matrix; toxins and reactive oxygen species
3. Activated stellate cells become fibrogenic **myofibroblasts**, stimulated by platelet-derived growth factor and **TGF-beta**
4. Extracellular matrix is deposited in the **space of Disse** [perisinusoidal space of the liver], with loss of sinusoidal endothelial fenestration (**sinusoidal capillarisation**)
5. Areas of hepatocyte loss are replaced by dense **fibrous septa** through collapse of the reticulin framework and collagen deposition by myofibroblasts
6. Septa encircle surviving hepatocytes, which replicate and form **regenerative nodules**
- If the injury stops (viral clearance, abstinence from alcohol), scarring stops and septa are broken down by metalloproteinases; vascular remodelling may persist, so portal hypertension may not resolve

## What are the complications of cirrhosis?

1. **Portal hypertension**, with its four major consequences: ascites, portosystemic venous shunts (oesophagogastric varices, haemorrhoids, caput medusae), congestive splenomegaly with hypersplenism, hepatic encephalopathy
2. **Hepatic encephalopathy**
3. **Coagulopathy** and **jaundice**
4. **Hepatorenal syndrome**
5. **Hepatopulmonary syndrome** and **portopulmonary hypertension**
6. **Bacterial infections** (damage to the gut mucosal barrier and Kupffer cell [macrophages lining the hepatic sinusoids] dysfunction)
7. **Hepatocellular carcinoma**
8. Pruritus from persistent cholestasis
9. Hyperoestrogenaemia: palmar erythema, spider angiomas; in males hypogonadism and gynaecomastia

## How is ascites formed in cirrhosis of liver?

- **Ascites** is accumulation of fluid in the peritoneal cavity; in 85% of cases it is caused by cirrhosis
- Pathogenesis is complex:
  - **Sinusoidal hypertension**
  - **Hypoalbuminaemia**
  - **Increased hepatic lymph flow**
  - **Splanchnic vasodilation and hyperdynamic circulation**
- Fluid is generally serous, with less than 3 g/dL of protein (largely albumin); clinically detectable when at least 500 mL has accumulated
- Neutrophils in the fluid suggest infection; red cells point to possible disseminated intra-abdominal cancer
- Long-standing ascites may produce right-sided hydrothorax through transdiaphragmatic lymphatics

## Write the mechanism of cirrhotic death.

The common causes of death in cirrhosis are:
1. **Hepatic encephalopathy**
2. **Bleeding from oesophageal varices**: varices appear in about 40% of individuals with advanced cirrhosis and cause massive haematemesis and death in about half of those affected
3. **Bacterial infections**, resulting from damage to the gut mucosal barrier and Kupffer cell [macrophages lining the hepatic sinusoids] dysfunction
4. **Hepatocellular carcinoma**
- Also: hepatorenal syndrome, coagulopathy

## A male has jaundice, gynaecomastia, testicular atrophy/abdominal distension, ascites. What is the likely diagnosis?

- **Cirrhosis of the liver** (chronic liver failure) with portal hypertension
- Jaundice and ascites reflect liver failure and portal hypertension
- Impaired oestrogen metabolism leads to **hyperoestrogenaemia**, which in males produces hypogonadism (testicular atrophy) and gynaecomastia; also palmar erythema and spider angiomas

## Same (40y): name the liver function tests expected to be altered.

| Test | Expected change |
|---|---|
| Serum **bilirubin**, total and direct; urine bilirubin | Increased |
| Serum **alkaline phosphatase**, GGT | Increased |
| **AST**, ALT, LDH | Increased |
| Serum **albumin** | Decreased |
| **Prothrombin time** and PTT (fibrinogen, prothrombin, factors V, VII, IX, X) | Prolonged |
| Serum **ammonia** | Increased |

## What is hepatic encephalopathy?

- A **spectrum of disturbances in consciousness**, ranging from subtle behavioural abnormalities, to marked confusion and stupor, to deep coma and death
- Fluctuating neurological signs: rigidity, hyperreflexia, **asterixis** (nonrhythmic, rapid extension and flexion movements of head and extremities, best seen with arms extended and wrists dorsiflexed)
- Believed to be caused by **elevated ammonia**, which correlates with impaired neuronal function and cerebral oedema
- Principal source of ammonia: the gastrointestinal tract, produced by microorganisms and enterocytes during glutamine metabolism
- Normally ammonia is carried in the portal vein to the liver and metabolised in the **urea cycle**; in severe liver disease this fails
- Neurotoxicity: direct effects on neurons, and conversion to **glutamine** in astrocytes, which acts as an osmolyte and contributes to oedema

## Enumerate the important causes of ascites.

- **Cirrhosis of the liver**: 85% of cases
- Other **portal hypertension**: prehepatic (portal vein thrombosis), intrahepatic (schistosomiasis, massive fatty change, sarcoidosis), posthepatic (right-sided heart failure, constrictive pericarditis, hepatic vein outflow obstruction)
- **Congestive heart failure**
- **Hypoalbuminaemia** (for example nephrotic syndrome)
- **Disseminated intra-abdominal cancer**
- Infection of the peritoneal fluid

## Why is UTI more common in females?

In the absence of instrumentation, urinary infections are much **more** common in females because of:

1. **Shorter urethra**
2. **Absence** of the antibacterial properties of prostatic fluid
3. **Hormonal changes affecting** bacterial adherence to the mucosa
4. **Urethral trauma during** sexual intercourse

## What is recurrent UTI?

Two episodes of acute bacterial cystitis with symptoms within 6 months, or three episodes within 1 year.

- **Relapse:** the same organism returns because it was incompletely eradicated; the interval between the two episodes is less than 2 weeks
- **Reinfection:** a new infection after the previous one was wholly eradicated, with a different organism
- More common in women; three-quarters are caused by Escherichia coli

## Write the importance of recurrent UTI.

- Recurrent infection is the setting in which **chronic pyelonephritis** develops: episodes of acute pyelonephritis recur because of predisposing factors such as **vesicoureteral reflux** and urinary tract obstruction
- Recurrent episodes leave irregular **scars** on the cortical surface, with tubular atrophy, interstitial fibrosis and deformity of the underlying calyx and pelvis
- Late presentation with gradual **renal insufficiency** and **hypertension**
- Persistent bacteria or infection with new organisms after treatment, and repeated septicaemic episodes when obstruction, diabetes or immunodeficiency is present
- Papillary necrosis may be superimposed and may lead to acute renal failure
- Men with recurrent cystitis should be evaluated for prostatitis

## What is pyelonephritis?

Inflammation caused by infection affecting the **tubules, interstitium and renal pelvis**.

## Describe the pathogenesis of ascending infection in pyelonephritis.

1. **Colonisation** of the distal urethra and introitus (in the female) by coliform bacteria, by adhesins on the P-fimbriae (pili) of bacteria that bind receptors on urothelial cells
2. **Entry into the bladder** during urethral catheterisation or other instrumentation; long-term catheterisation carries a particular risk
3. **Movement from the bladder to the kidney**, favoured by:
   - **Urinary tract obstruction and stasis of urine:** residual urine lets bacteria multiply unhindered; seen with benign prostatic hypertrophy, tumours, calculi and neurogenic bladder (diabetes, spinal cord injury)
   - **Vesicoureteral reflux:** an incompetent vesicoureteral valve lets bladder urine and bacteria ascend the ureter into the renal pelvis; usually due to congenital absence or shortening of the intravesical ureter, and present in about 30% of infants and children with urinary tract infection
   - **Intrarenal reflux:** infected urine is propelled through open ducts at the papillary tips deep into the parenchyma, most often at the upper and lower poles where papillae are flat or concave
- Without vesicoureteral reflux the infection usually stays in the bladder, causing cystitis and urethritis

## What are the complications of acute pyelonephritis?

1. **Papillary necrosis:** seen mainly in diabetics, sickle cell disease and urinary tract obstruction; coagulative necrosis of the tips or distal two-thirds of the pyramids; may lead to acute renal failure
2. **Pyonephrosis:** total or almost complete obstruction, high in the urinary tract; pus fills the renal pelvis, calyces and ureter
3. **Perinephric abscess:** suppurative inflammation extends through the renal capsule into the perinephric tissue
- Repeated septicaemic episodes in the presence of unrelieved obstruction, diabetes or immunodeficiency
- Healing leaves irregular cortical scars

## Same: how will you confirm it?

- **Imaging:** computed tomography or magnetic resonance imaging detects the renal mass; many tumours are now found incidentally on these studies
- **Histology:** the tumour is typed by its histologic, cytogenetic and genetic features
  - Clear cell (70% to 80%), papillary (10% to 15%), chromophobe (5%), Xp11 translocation and collecting duct carcinoma
- Spread is looked for: renal vein invasion with a column of tumour towards the inferior vena cava, and metastases to the lungs (more than 50%) and bones (33%)

## Write a short note on haematuria.

**Definition.** Haematuria is the abnormal presence of blood in the urine. It is **gross** (visible blood) or **microscopic** (red blood cells found only on microscopic urinalysis, with normal-looking urine).

**Causes.** Glomerular or nonglomerular (see the causes of haematuria).

**Features**

- **Gross:** red, pink or tea-coloured urine
- **Microscopic haematuria:** 3 or more red blood cells per high-power field on microscopy of the urine
- Glomerular bleeding shows **proteinuria**, **dysmorphic red cells** or **red cell casts**
- Nonglomerular bleeding shows normal red cells, without proteinuria or casts

**Investigation**

- **Urinalysis** is the first test; a positive dipstick must be confirmed by microscopy of the urine sediment
- Absence of red cells in a strongly dipstick-positive sample suggests myoglobinuria or haemoglobinuria
- Cystoscopy and CT urogram are used to exclude **urothelial or renal malignancy**
- A renal biopsy is needed when a glomerular source is likely

**Importance.** Haematuria is the presenting symptom in most bladder cancers and is often the earliest sign of a renal neoplasm, so malignancy must always be excluded.

## Enumerate the important causes of haematuria.

**Glomerular**

- Alport syndrome [inherited type IV collagen defect with nephritis, deafness and eye changes]
- IgA nephropathy (Berger disease [IgA nephropathy])
- Poststreptococcal (postinfectious) glomerulonephritis
- Crescentic and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis
- Lupus nephritis
- Goodpasture syndrome [anti-glomerular basement membrane disease]
- Thin basement membrane disease
- Polycystic kidney disease

**Nonglomerular**

- Urinary tract infection (cystitis, urethritis, prostatitis)
- Urolithiasis and nephrolithiasis
- Bladder and urothelial cancer, renal cell carcinoma, prostate cancer
- Benign prostatic hyperplasia
- Trauma and instrumentation of the urinary tract
- Haemorrhagic cystitis and radiation cystitis
- Sickle cell disease (papillary necrosis), thrombocytopenia and coagulopathies
- Drugs such as cyclophosphamide and ifosfamide
- Schistosomiasis in endemic regions
- Strenuous exercise

## Write a short note on microscopic haematuria.

**Definition.** Microscopic haematuria is the detection of 3 or more red blood cells per high-power field on microscopic urinalysis, without an apparent cause, while the urine looks normal.

**Types**

| Type | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Asymptomatic, no proteinuria** | Normal red cells; possible urothelial malignancy |
| **Asymptomatic with proteinuria** | Dysmorphic red cells; glomerular disease |
| **Symptomatic** | Infection, prostatitis or benign prostatic hyperplasia |

**Investigation**

- A dipstick alone does not make the diagnosis; **microscopy of the urine sediment** must confirm it
- Blood from recent urological procedures, infection, or rectal or vaginal bleeding must be excluded
- Patients are placed in low, intermediate or high risk groups for urothelial cancer by age, smoking history and the number of red cells per high-power field
- Higher risk patients need **cystoscopy** and a **CT urogram**; a renal biopsy may be needed in glomerular haematuria

## What do you mean by massive proteinuria?

Daily loss of **3.5 g or more of protein** in the urine (less in children), the feature of nephrotic syndrome.

## What are the causes of massive proteinuria?

Massive proteinuria is caused by derangement of the glomerular capillary wall, which increases permeability to plasma proteins, as in nephrotic syndrome.

- **Primary glomerular lesions:** minimal change disease (most common in children), membranous nephropathy (most common in older adults), primary focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (all ages)
- **Systemic causes:** diabetes, amyloidosis and systemic lupus erythematosus are the most frequent
- Less common: membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis and IgA nephropathy
- Secondary membranous nephropathy: drugs (penicillamine, captopril, gold, NSAIDs), carcinomas of lung and colon and melanoma, systemic lupus erythematosus, infections (chronic hepatitis B, hepatitis C, syphilis, schistosomiasis, malaria) and thyroiditis

## Name the tests we do for proteinuria.

1. **Urine dipstick** for protein
2. **24-hour urine protein** collection
3. Random urine protein to creatinine ratio; an early morning sample is preferred as it represents the 24-hour sample
4. **Urine albumin**, in a 24-hour collection or as an early morning or random albumin to creatinine ratio

## What are the causes of proteinuria?

- **Glomerular proteinuria:** defect in permselectivity of the glomerular filtration barrier, as in glomerulonephritis and nephrotic syndrome
- **Tubular proteinuria:** incomplete tubular reabsorption of protein, as in interstitial nephritis
- **Overflow proteinuria:** increased plasma concentration of protein, as in multiple myeloma (Bence Jones protein [free monoclonal light chains in the urine]) and myoglobinuria
- Urinary tract inflammation or tumour
- Conditions not related to renal disease: posture, fever, exercise, and urinary tract infection

## How do you perform the heat coagulation test of urine?

**Principle.** Heat in an acid medium denatures and precipitates the proteins of the urine, which appear as turbidity.

**Procedure**

1. Fill about three-fourths of a test tube with clear urine
2. Heat the upper part of the urine over a flame, keeping the lower part unheated as a control
3. Add 3 to 5 drops of dilute (3%) acetic acid

**Interpretation**

- **Turbidity that persists or increases:** protein is present (**positive**)
- **Turbidity that disappears:** it was due to phosphates and carbonates, which acetic acid dissolves (**negative**)

## What is microalbumin? Write its importance.

- **Microalbumin** is an obsolete term, as there is no such biochemical molecule; it is now called **urine albumin**
- Marker for the detection of incipient nephropathy in diabetics
- Independent marker for cardiovascular disease, since it indicates increased endothelial permeability
- Marker of chronic renal impairment
- Measured in a 24-hour urine or as an early morning or random albumin to creatinine ratio
- Albuminuria on two occasions, with urinary tract infection excluded, indicates glomerular dysfunction; albuminuria for 3 months or more indicates chronic kidney disease

## Give the normal serum concentration of uric acid.

- Adult men and postmenopausal women: **3.5 to 7.2 mg/dL**
- Premenopausal women: **2.6 to 6.0 mg/dL**

## Give the normal serum albumin value.

**3.5 to 5.0 g/dL**

## Give the normal serum urea value.

Blood urea nitrogen: **8 to 20 mg/dL**, with levels up to 23 mg/dL regarded as normal in those over 60 years.

## Give the normal serum creatinine value.

- Males: **0.6 to 1.2 mg/dL**
- Females: **0.4 to 1.0 mg/dL**

## Name the important renal function tests with interpretation and normal values.

| Test | Normal | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| **Glomerular filtration rate (GFR)** | 90 to 120 mL per minute in an adult male | The best overall indicator of glomerular function; less than 15 mL per minute is end-stage renal failure; a normal GFR does not exclude renal disease |
| **Creatinine clearance** | Calculated from urinary creatinine, urine flow rate and plasma creatinine | Indicator of GFR; overestimates GFR by 10% to 20% because of tubular secretion; needs accurate timed urine collection |
| **Serum creatinine** | Males 0.6 to 1.2 mg/dL; females 0.4 to 1.0 mg/dL | Raised when GFR falls or urine elimination is obstructed; a late marker, since about 50% of kidney function is lost before it rises |
| **Blood urea nitrogen** | 8 to 20 mg/dL | Raised in acute and chronic renal impairment, but also in upper gastrointestinal bleeding, dehydration, catabolic states and high protein diet; rises earlier but is less accurate than creatinine |
| **BUN to creatinine ratio** | Not applicable | About 20 to 1 in pre-renal disease; about 10 to 1 in intrinsic renal disease |
| **Cystatin C** | Not given | Inversely related to GFR; not affected by age, muscle bulk or diet; more reliable than creatinine in early renal impairment |
| **Urine albumin and protein** | Protein up to 150 mg per day; albumin to creatinine ratio less than 30 mg per g | Frank proteinuria is more than 300 mg per day; albumin to creatinine ratio of 30 to 300 mg per g (A2) and more than 300 mg per g (A3) are abnormal; albuminuria for 3 months or more indicates chronic kidney disease |
| **Urine specific gravity and osmolality** | Specific gravity 1.003 to 1.030; osmolality more than 750 mOsmol per kg | Tests of tubular concentrating ability |

## What do you mean by renal clearance test?

- The rate in mL per minute at which a substance is removed from the plasma by the kidney
- **Clearance** equals urinary concentration multiplied by urinary flow rate (volume per time), divided by plasma concentration
- The **glomerular filtration rate** is the clearance of a substance that is freely filtered and neither reabsorbed nor secreted by the tubule
  - Inulin clearance is the reference method; radioisotopes and iohexol are other exogenous markers
  - **Creatinine clearance** is the most commonly used endogenous marker; it needs a 24-hour urine collection, or preferably an accurately timed collection of 5 to 8 hours, and is corrected for body surface area
- Normal GFR in an adult male is 90 to 120 mL per minute

## Write about the causes of uremia.

Uraemia is azotaemia that has led to clinical signs and symptoms, so it arises from renal failure.

- **Acute kidney injury:** rapid decline in GFR over hours to days, from glomerular, interstitial, vascular or acute tubular injury
  - Pre-renal causes: reduced blood flow to the kidneys, as in hypotensive and cardiogenic shock, dehydration and major blood loss
  - Renal causes: nephrotoxic drugs and toxins, sepsis, myeloma, autoimmune and inflammatory disease, tubular damage
  - Post-renal causes: blockage of the urinary tract by bladder, prostate or cervical cancer, large stones and blood clots
- **Chronic kidney disease:** GFR persistently less than 60 mL per minute per 1.73 square metres for at least 3 months and/or persistent albuminuria; hypertension and diabetes are the most common causes worldwide
- **End-stage kidney disease:** GFR less than 5% of normal, the terminal stage of uraemia

## Why is serum creatinine more specific than blood urea?

- Creatinine is the by-product of creatine phosphate in muscle and is produced at a constant rate
- It is cleared from the blood almost entirely by the kidney
- **Urea** is formed in the liver from protein metabolism and only about 85% is eliminated by the kidneys
- Urea also rises in upper gastrointestinal bleeding, dehydration, catabolic states and high protein diet, and falls in starvation, low protein diet and severe liver disease
- Serum creatinine is therefore the more accurate assessment of renal function, although urea rises earlier in renal disease

## Give the clinical importance of serum creatinine.

- Raised when there is a significant reduction in GFR or when urine elimination is obstructed
- A **late marker**: about 50% of kidney function is lost before the level rises
- Used in the estimating equations for GFR (MDRD and CKD-EPI), which are superior to serum creatinine alone as they include age, gender and race
- Used to stage chronic kidney disease and to define acute kidney injury
- Used with BUN to give the BUN to creatinine ratio, which separates pre-renal from renal causes
- Limitations: raised by high protein intake and increased muscle bulk; unreliable in the elderly, amputees and muscular dystrophy; falls in pregnancy

## State the clinical significance of raised serum uric acid.

- Most people with hyperuricaemia are **asymptomatic** (85% to 90%)
- Raised uric acid in the blood or urine can lead to **gout**, which is its most common manifestation
- It can lead to **nephrolithiasis** (uric acid stones)
- It can cause **acute uric acid nephropathy**, from precipitation of uric acid crystals in the renal tubules
- Uric acid levels can be raised 10 to 15 years before the clinical onset of gout

## Name the causes of hyperuricaemia.

**Overproduction** (about 10% of cases)

- Increased breakdown of purines
- Fructose, whose hepatic metabolism produces uric acid

**Underexcretion** (about 90% of cases)

- Decreased glomerular filtration, as in **chronic kidney disease**
- Reduced tubular secretion and increased tubular reabsorption of urate
- **Drugs:** thiazide and loop diuretics, low-dose aspirin, ciclosporin
- Alcohol, which increases production and decreases excretion
- Lead poisoning

## What is renal glycosuria?

Glycosuria is the presence of reducing sugars in the urine. **Renal glycosuria** is glucose in the urine with a normal blood glucose level, because of an isolated defect of glucose reabsorption in the proximal tubule of the nephron.

- It is often found as an incidental finding on routine urine testing in otherwise healthy people
- **Familial renal glucosuria** is caused by mutation of the **SLC5A2** gene, which codes for the sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (**SGLT2**)

## Why is sugar present in renal glycosuria?

- Glucose is filtered at the glomerulus and is normally reabsorbed completely in the proximal tubule
- In renal glycosuria the tubule cannot reabsorb the filtered glucose, so glucose appears in the urine although the **blood glucose is normal**
- The usual cause is a mutation of the **SLC5A2** gene, which codes for the **SGLT2** transporter
- Tubular absorption of glucose is also impaired in **Fanconi syndrome** [generalised proximal tubular reabsorption defect], where phosphate and amino acids are lost as well

## Name the routine tests/investigations done on urine.

1. **Physical examination:** colour and clarity
   - Normal urine is straw-coloured; dark in dehydration; red in haematuria, porphyria or after beetroot; cloudy in pyuria
   - Specific gravity, 1.003 to 1.030
2. **Chemical examination** by dipstick: protein, glucose, blood, ketones, bilirubin, urobilinogen, nitrite and leucocyte esterase
3. **Microscopic examination:** cells, casts, crystals and micro-organisms
- A freshly voided midstream sample is best, as it is less likely to be contaminated by epithelial cells and commensal bacteria

## Mention the chemical examinations done in urine with their principles.

The urine dipstick uses **dry chemistry**: reagents impregnated on the paper change colour on contact with urine, and the colour is compared with a chart.

| Analyte | Interpretation |
|---|---|
| **Protein** | Not detectable in healthy urine |
| **Glucose** | Not detected in health; seen in diabetes mellitus, pregnancy and renal glycosuria, when the renal threshold of 180 mg/dL is lowered |
| **Blood** | Detects the globin portion of haemoglobin, so cannot separate haemoglobin from myoglobin; ascorbic acid causes a falsely negative result |
| **Ketones** | Present in fasting, severe vomiting and diabetic ketoacidosis; detects acetoacetate and acetone only |
| **Bilirubin** | Not detected in normal urine; present in conjugated hyperbilirubinaemia |
| **Urobilinogen** | May be present normally; absent in conjugated hyperbilirubinaemia, increased in pre-hepatic jaundice and haemolysis |
| **Nitrite and leucocyte esterase** | Indicators of urinary tract infection; Enterobacteriaceae convert nitrate to nitrite |

## What are the reducing substances/agents found in urine?

Reducing substances reduce cupric ions in an alkaline solution to cuprous ions (cuprous oxide), as in **Benedict's test** [copper-reduction test for reducing sugars].

- **Reducing sugars:** glucose, fructose, galactose and lactose
- **Reducing non-sugars:** ascorbic acid, uric acid and urates, glucuronides, creatinine, salicylates, streptomycin, homogentisic acid, formaldehyde
- **Sucrose** is a nonreducing sugar and gives a negative result

## How do you perform Benedict's test and interpret it?

**Principle.** Reducing sugars reduce the blue cupric ions of Benedict's [copper-reduction test for reducing sugars] reagent, in alkaline medium and on heating, to red **cuprous oxide**.

**Procedure**

1. Take 5 mL of Benedict's qualitative reagent in a test tube
2. Add 8 drops (0.5 mL) of urine
3. Boil for 2 minutes over a flame or in a boiling water bath
4. Let it cool and note the colour

**Interpretation**

| Colour | Grade | Sugar |
|---|---|---|
| Blue, no change | Negative | None |
| Green solution | Trace | Less than 0.5 g/dL |
| Green precipitate | 1 plus | 0.5 to 1 g/dL |
| Yellow precipitate | 2 plus | 1 to 1.5 g/dL |
| Orange precipitate | 3 plus | 1.5 to 2 g/dL |
| Brick-red precipitate | 4 plus | More than 2 g/dL |

- The test detects any reducing substance, so it is **not specific for glucose**
- Creatinine, ascorbic acid and urate in concentrated urine can interfere with the reaction

## Define oliguria.

Urine output of **less than 400 mL per day**.

## Mention the causes of oliguria.

- **Pre-renal:** reduced renal blood flow, as in hypotensive and cardiogenic shock, dehydration and major blood loss
- **Renal:** acute tubular injury, nephrotoxic drugs, sepsis, myeloma, autoimmune disease; nephritic syndrome and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis
- **Post-renal:** blockage of the urinary tract; complete bilateral obstruction of rapid onset leads to oliguria or anuria

## Classify testicular (germ cell) tumours.

Germ cell tumours (95% of testicular neoplasms) are in two groups, with the other main category being sex cord–stromal tumours.

1. Derived from germ cell neoplasia in situ
   - Pure forms: **seminoma**, embryonal carcinoma, yolk sac tumour, choriocarcinoma, teratoma (postpubertal)
   - Mixed germ cell tumours (more than one histologic type; 30% to 40% of germ cell tumours)
2. Unrelated to germ cell neoplasia in situ
   - Spermatocytic tumour
   - Prepubertal-type teratoma and yolk sac tumour, and mixed teratoma with yolk sac tumour of prepubertal type
- Clinically they are divided into **seminomas** and nonseminomatous germ cell tumours

## Name the testicular tumour markers.

- **Alpha-fetoprotein (AFP):** raised by yolk sac tumour elements
- **Human chorionic gonadotropin (hCG):** raised by syncytiotrophoblastic elements

## Mention the uses of testicular tumour markers.

1. **Initial evaluation** of testicular masses
2. **Staging**: after orchiectomy, persistent elevation of hCG or AFP indicates metastatic spread
3. **Assessing tumour burden**: markers are incorporated into the TNM stage for prognostic stage grouping
4. **Monitoring the response** to therapy

## Name the tumour associated with increased beta-hCG.

- **Choriocarcinoma** (syncytiotrophoblasts contain hCG)
- Also **seminoma** with syncytiotrophoblasts (about 15%), where the rise is minimal
- Raised hCG or AFP in more than 80% of nonseminomatous germ cell tumours at diagnosis

## Write down the morphology of seminoma (short note).

**Gross**
- Bulky masses, sometimes 10 times the size of the normal testis
- Homogeneous, grey-white, lobulated cut surface, usually without haemorrhage or necrosis
- Tunica albuginea generally not penetrated; occasional extension to the epididymis, spermatic cord or scrotal sac

**Microscopy**
- Sheets of uniform cells divided into poorly demarcated lobules by delicate fibrous septa containing a lymphocytic infiltrate; ill-defined granulomas in some
- **Classic seminoma cell:** round to polyhedral, distinct cell membrane, clear or watery cytoplasm containing glycogen, large central nucleus with one or two prominent nucleoli
- About 15% contain **syncytiotrophoblasts**, with a mild rise in serum hCG
- Immunohistochemistry: positive for KIT, OCT3/4 and podoplanin; negative for cytokeratin

## How does seminoma differ from non-seminomatous germ cell tumour?

| Feature | Seminoma | Nonseminomatous germ cell tumour |
|---|---|---|
| **Cells** | Resemble primordial germ cells or early gonocytes | Undifferentiated cells resembling embryonic stem cells (embryonal carcinoma), or differentiation into yolk sac tumour, choriocarcinoma and teratoma |
| **Cut surface** | Homogeneous, grey-white, usually no haemorrhage or necrosis | Variegated, with haemorrhage or necrosis (embryonal carcinoma) |
| **Local spread** | Tunica albuginea generally not penetrated | Embryonal carcinoma often extends through the tunica albuginea into the epididymis or spermatic cord |
| **Markers** | hCG minimally raised in about 15% | AFP or hCG raised in more than 80% |
| **Metastasis** | Mainly to lymph nodes; blood spread later | Earlier and more frequent blood spread, as well as lymphatic |
| **Treatment response** | Radiosensitive and chemosensitive | Aggressive chemotherapy needed |
| **Prognosis** | Best; more than 95% of stage I and II cured | About 90% complete remission; pure choriocarcinoma poor |

## How will you investigate a case of testicular mass/tumour in the laboratory?

1. **Serum tumour markers:** AFP and hCG, in the initial evaluation
   - Marked rise of AFP indicates yolk sac tumour; of hCG indicates syncytiotrophoblastic elements
2. **Histology** of the radical orchiectomy specimen
   - Any solid testicular mass is considered neoplastic until proven otherwise
   - Biopsy is avoided because of the risk of tumour spillage
   - Intraoperative frozen section may be done when a benign lesion is suspected and partial orchiectomy is possible
3. Markers after orchiectomy: persistent elevation indicates metastatic spread

## A 30y man has painless testicular swelling. What is your provisional diagnosis?

**Germ cell tumour of the testis**, most likely seminoma.

- Germ cell tumours are the most common cancer in males between 15 and 45 years of age
- Painless enlargement of the testis is the characteristic feature
- Seminoma is the most common germ cell tumour (about 50%), with peak incidence in the fourth decade
- Any solid testicular mass is considered neoplastic until proven otherwise

## What is cryptorchidism?

Complete or partial failure of the intra-abdominal testes (one or both) to descend into the scrotal sac.

- Found in about 1% of 1-year-old boys; bilateral in 25%
- Most common arrest site is the inguinal canal; arrest within the abdomen accounts for 5% to 10%
- Associated with testicular dysfunction and an increased risk of testicular cancer (four- to 10-fold, not removed by orchiopexy)
- Other associations: hypospadias, Prader-Willi syndrome [imprinting disorder from loss of paternal 15q11-13 with hypotonia and obesity], Noonan syndrome [RAS-pathway disorder with webbed neck and pulmonary stenosis]
- **Morphology:** small, firm testis; thickened tubular basement membrane, loss of spermatogonia leaving only Sertoli cells [supporting cell of the seminiferous tubule], hyalinised scarred tubules, prominent Leydig cells [testosterone-secreting interstitial cell of the testis]; germ cell neoplasia in situ may be present
- **Clinical features:** empty scrotal sac; sterility; inguinal hernia (10% to 20%); crushing injury
- Most inguinal testes descend spontaneously in the first year; otherwise orchiopexy at 6 to 12 months of age

## Write a short note on benign prostatic hyperplasia (70y male with prostatism: likely diagnosis).

**Benign prostatic hyperplasia (nodular hyperplasia)** is the most common benign prostatic disease in males older than 50 years; histologic evidence is found in up to 90% of males by age 80 years. It is not a premalignant lesion. The likely diagnosis in a 70-year-old male with urinary obstructive symptoms is benign prostatic hyperplasia.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**
- **Dihydrotestosterone (DHT)** is the main androgen in the prostate, formed from testosterone by type 2 5-alpha-reductase in stromal cells
- DHT binds androgen receptors and switches on androgen-dependent genes for growth factors, chiefly **fibroblast growth factor** family members and **transforming growth factor beta**
- The ultimate cause is unknown

**Morphology**
- Prostate weight rises three- to five-fold (60 to 100 g) or more
- Affects the **transition zone** and compresses the urethra to a slit-like orifice
- Nodules rich in glands are yellow-pink, soft and exude milky prostatic fluid; nodules of fibromuscular stroma are pale grey and firm
- Microscopy: small to large to cystically dilated glands separated by bland spindle-shaped stromal cells; glands lined by an inner columnar secretory layer and an outer cuboidal or flattened basal layer
- Prostatic infarcts may occur, with adjacent squamous metaplasia

**Clinical features**
- Urinary frequency, nocturia, difficulty in starting and stopping the stream, overflow dribbling and dysuria
- Sudden acute urinary retention may need emergency catheterisation

**Complications**
- Bladder muscular wall hypertrophy and distension, with trabeculation
- Residual urine, a common site of infection; increased risk of bacterial infection of the bladder and kidney

## Mention the important diagnostic workup for BPH.

1. **History** with the International Prostate Symptom Score (IPSS)
2. **Physical examination:** abdominal examination for bladder enlargement; digital rectal examination for prostate size, consistency and tenderness
3. **Urinalysis** for glucosuria, proteinuria, haematuria and infection
4. **Serum prostate-specific antigen (PSA):** not required routinely; used after shared decision-making
5. **Post-void residual urine** and ultrasound for prostate volume and residual urine, when needed

## What are the common hormones done in male and female to evaluate infertility?

**In the male**

- **Serum total testosterone** (8 AM to 10 AM) and **follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH)** are the first tests
- If testosterone is low, repeat total and free testosterone and add **luteinising hormone (LH)** and **prolactin**
- Other pituitary hormones are checked when the picture suggests hypogonadotropic hypogonadism: free T4, morning cortisol and prolactin

| Pattern | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Low testosterone, high FSH, high LH | Primary testicular failure |
| Normal testosterone, normal LH, high FSH | Seminiferous tubule damage without Leydig cell [testosterone-secreting interstitial cell of the testis] dysfunction |
| Low testosterone, low or normal FSH and LH | Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism; high prolactin suggests a prolactin-secreting tumour |
| High testosterone, high LH, normal FSH | Partial androgen insensitivity |

## How will you investigate a case of male infertility in the laboratory?

1. **Semen analysis:** volume, pH, liquefaction, viscosity, sperm number, motility, vitality and morphology
2. **Post-ejaculation urine** for sperm when semen volume is low, to confirm retrograde ejaculation
3. **Tests for sperm antibodies** when sperm agglutination is seen: mixed antiglobulin reaction test and immunobead test
4. **Seminal markers:** fructose, zinc and neutral glucosidase
5. **Peroxidase staining** and leucocyte markers on round cells; more than 1 million leucocytes per mL (**pyospermia**) needs a search for genital tract infection
6. **Hormones:** serum total testosterone, FSH, LH and prolactin
7. **Genetic tests:** karyotyping for Klinefelter syndrome [47,XXY] and Y chromosome microdeletion; **CFTR** gene testing in congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens

## Mention the indications of semen analysis.

- Assessment of **male factor infertility**
- Planning of assisted fertilisation

## How is semen collected?

1. Collected by the man by **masturbation**, at home or near the laboratory, after a minimum of 3 days and a maximum of 7 days of abstinence
2. The **whole ejaculate** must be collected; any loss of the sample is reported to the laboratory
3. Collected into a **clean, wide-mouthed container** that is nontoxic to spermatozoa and kept at 20 to 37 °C
4. If masturbation is not possible, semen can be collected in a **nontoxic condom** during intercourse; latex condoms are not used because they affect sperm viability and motility
5. Delivered to the laboratory **within 1 hour** of collection
6. Two or three samples are advisable, because semen composition varies

## Mention the normal findings of semen.

| Parameter | Lower reference limit |
|---|---|
| **Volume** | More than 1.5 mL |
| **pH** | More than 7.2 |
| **Total sperm number** | 39 million per ejaculate or more |
| **Normal forms (Tygerberg method)** | More than 4% |
| **Vitality** | More than 58% live sperm |
| **Progressive motility** | More than 32% |
| **Total motility (progressive and nonprogressive)** | More than 40% |
| **Agglutination** | None |
| **Viscosity** | Less than 2 cm after liquefaction |

- Seminal fructose more than 13 micromoles per ejaculate
- Seminal zinc more than 2.4 micromoles per ejaculate
- Seminal neutral glucosidase less than 20 milliunits per ejaculate

## What is oligospermia?

**Oligospermia** (oligozoospermia) is semen with a reduced sperm concentration: less than 15 million per mL, or a total sperm count of less than 39 million.

## What is azoospermia?

**Azoospermia** is semen without spermatozoa. It is diagnosed only after the ejaculate has been **centrifuged** and the sperm pellet examined under the microscope.

## Write down the causes of azoospermia.

1. **Hypothalamic-pituitary failure** (hypogonadotropic hypogonadism): other pituitary hormone deficiencies, prolactin-secreting tumour
2. **Primary testicular failure:** Klinefelter syndrome [47,XXY], Y chromosome microdeletion, radiation or chemotherapy, testicular trauma, torsion of the testes, mumps
3. **Obstruction of the genital tract:**
   - Congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens, often with **CFTR** mutations (cystic fibrosis)
   - Ejaculatory duct obstruction from chlamydia, gonorrhoea, tuberculosis or vasectomy
4. Transient azoospermia can follow toxic, environmental, infectious or iatrogenic insults

## Write a short note on semen analysis.

**Semen analysis** is the laboratory test used to evaluate male fertility. Semen is a mixture of spermatozoa from the testes, processed in the epididymides and mixed with secretions of the prostate, seminal vesicles and bulbourethral glands.

**Procedure**

1. Liquefaction at 37 °C, which may take up to 60 minutes
2. Measurement of volume, then pH
3. Wet preparation for appearance, motility and agglutination; motility is tested within 1 hour of collection
4. Vitality test, especially when sperm number is low
5. Dilution and counting of sperm
6. Smears for morphology

**Interpretation**

- **Low volume** (less than 1.5 mL): incomplete collection, retrograde ejaculation, testosterone deficiency, ejaculatory duct obstruction or absent vas deferens and seminal vesicles
- **Agglutination:** test for anti-sperm antibodies
- **Many immotile and nonviable sperm:** epididymal pathology; immotile but viable sperm suggest a flagellar defect
- **Abnormal morphology:** a problem of spermatogenesis
- Abnormal results are repeated after 3 months, one full cycle of spermatogenesis

## Write down the risk factors for carcinoma of cervix.

- **Persistent infection with high-risk HPV**, the most important factor; HPV-16 accounts for almost 60% of cases and HPV-18 for another 10%
- Young age at first intercourse
- Multiple sexual partners
- Male partner with multiple sexual partners
- Co-carcinogens and host immune status, which decide whether an HPV infection regresses or persists
- Lack of participation in regular screening: more than one-half of invasive cancers occur in females who were not screened

## Describe briefly the pathogenesis of carcinoma cervix.

1. **High-risk HPV infection** of immature basal squamous cells, at sites of squamous trauma and repair and in the immature metaplastic cells of the transformation zone
2. Most infections are transient (50% cleared within 8 months, 90% within 2 years); **persistent infection** leads to precursor lesions
3. Viral **E6** binds p53 and promotes its degradation; E6 also upregulates telomerase, which leads to immortalisation
4. Viral **E7** promotes degradation of RB and inhibits p21 and p27, so cells progress through the cell cycle and cannot repair DNA damage
5. **Integration** of viral DNA into the host genome increases expression of E6 and E7 and may dysregulate nearby oncogenes such as MYC
6. Additional mutations accumulate; low-grade lesion progresses to high-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion and then invasive carcinoma, over decades

## State the role of HPV in causing carcinoma of cervix.

- High-risk HPVs are by far the most important factor; 15 high-risk types are known
- HPV-16 accounts for almost 60% of cases, HPV-18 for another 10%
- More than 80% of low-grade and 100% of high-grade squamous intraepithelial lesions are associated with high-risk HPV
- Carcinogenic action depends on viral **E6** (binds and degrades p53; upregulates telomerase) and **E7** (degrades RB; inhibits p21 and p27)
- Viral DNA is integrated into the host genome in most cancers, increasing E6 and E7 expression
- Only a few infected females develop cancer, so co-carcinogens and host immune status also matter

## What is CIN?

**Cervical intraepithelial neoplasia**, the precursor lesion of cervical squamous carcinoma, graded by dysplasia:

| CIN grade | Dysplasia | Current term |
|---|---|---|
| **CIN 1** | Mild | Low-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion (LSIL) |
| **CIN 2** | Moderate | High-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion (HSIL) |
| **CIN 3** | Severe, carcinoma in situ | High-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion (HSIL) |

- Grading depends on how far immature cells expand from the basal layer: lower one-third in LSIL; upper two-thirds in HSIL
- Nuclear atypia with perinuclear halos (**koilocytic atypia**)

## Mention the diagnostic tools of cervical cancer.

1. **Pap test** [cervical cytology screening] (cervical cytology) from the transformation zone
2. **HPV DNA testing** of the cervical scrape: higher sensitivity but lower specificity than the Pap test
3. **Colposcopy** after acetic acid, which shows abnormal epithelium as aceto-white areas
4. **Biopsy** of abnormal areas for histology; Ki-67 and p16 immunohistochemistry confirm equivocal squamous intraepithelial lesions
5. **Cervical conisation** treats high-grade lesions and superficially invasive carcinoma

## Write a short note on Pap smear.

- Cytologic screening for cervical precursor lesions and low-stage cancer; credited with a 75% fall in cervical cancer death rate
- **Technique:** the transformation zone of the cervix is circumferentially scraped with a spatula or brush, and the cells are smeared or spun down onto a slide; the smear is fixed and stained by the Papanicolaou method [cervical cytology screening], then screened microscopically or by automated image analysis
- **Findings:** the spectrum from low-grade to high-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion
- **Schedule:** first smear at 21 years or within 3 years of onset of sexual activity, then every 3 years; after 30 years with normal cytology and negative HPV, every 5 years by HPV testing alone or with a Pap test [cervical cytology screening]
- An abnormal result is followed by colposcopy and biopsy

## A female has PV bleeding/discharge, contact bleeding, an ulcerated/friable/fungating cervical growth. What is the likely diagnosis?

**Invasive carcinoma of the cervix**, most likely **squamous cell carcinoma**.

- The average age is 45 to 50 years
- Squamous cell carcinoma accounts for about 80% of cases; adenocarcinoma for about 15%
- All are caused by **high-risk HPV**
- It appears as a **fungating (exophytic)** or infiltrative mass
- More than one-half of cases are found in females who did not attend regular screening

## Same: what do you expect in histopathology?

- **Squamous cell carcinoma** (about 80%): nests and tongues of malignant squamous epithelium, keratinising or nonkeratinising, invading the cervical stroma
- **Adenocarcinoma** (about 15%): proliferating malignant endocervical glands with large hyperchromatic nuclei and mucin-depleted cytoplasm
- Adenosquamous and neuroendocrine carcinomas (remaining 5%)
- Preceding high-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion shows nuclear atypia and koilocytes

## Same: name the microbial agent/oncogenic virus associated with it.

**High-risk human papillomavirus**, mainly HPV-16 (almost 60%) and HPV-18 (about 10%).

## Same: how will you confirm it / which investigations are needed?

1. **Biopsy** of the lesion, with histology: the diagnosis is made on tissue
2. **Pap test** [cervical cytology screening] and **HPV DNA testing** of the cervical scrape
3. **Colposcopy** with acetic acid, then biopsy of abnormal areas
4. **Staging:** stage 0 carcinoma in situ; stage I confined to the cervix; stage II beyond the cervix but not the pelvic wall, and not the lower third of the vagina; stage III lower third of vagina, pelvic wall, hydronephrosis or nodes; stage IV beyond the true pelvis or bladder or rectal mucosa

## Write a short note on endometriosis.

**Definition:** endometrial glands and stroma outside the uterus; the ectopic tissue may bleed cyclically.

**Pathogenesis**
1. **Regurgitation theory:** retrograde flow of menstrual endometrium
2. **Benign metastasis theory:** spread through blood vessels and lymphatics to distant sites such as bone, lung and brain
3. **Metaplastic theory:** from coelomic epithelium, or from mesonephric remnants
4. **Extrauterine stem or progenitor cell theory:** from bone marrow cells
- Implants release proinflammatory and angiogenic factors, and stromal cells produce aromatase, so oestrogen enhances their survival

**Morphology**
- Red-blue to yellow-brown nodules on or beneath mucosal or serosal surfaces
- Organising haemorrhage causes fibrous adhesions between tubes and ovaries and obliterates the pouch of Douglas
- Ovarian **chocolate cysts (endometriomas)**, 3 to 5 cm, filled with brown fluid
- Microscopy: endometrial glands and stroma, with or without haemosiderin

**Clinical features and complications**
- Severe dysmenorrhoea, dyspareunia and pelvic pain; menstrual irregularity
- Infertility in 30% to 40%
- Malignancy (endometrioid and clear cell carcinoma) may arise in endometriotic cysts

## A 60y woman has postmenopausal bleeding with thick endometrium on USG. What is the provisional diagnosis?

**Endometrial carcinoma**, most likely endometrioid type; endometrial hyperplasia is the other important cause.

- Peak incidence in postmenopausal females 55 to 65 years of age
- Usually produces irregular or postmenopausal vaginal bleeding
- Endometrial hyperplasia, an important cause of abnormal bleeding, is a frequent precursor and follows prolonged oestrogenic stimulation (obesity, polycystic ovarian syndrome, granulosa cell tumour, oestrogen therapy)

## Same: how can you confirm it?

The diagnosis must be established by **histologic examination of tissue obtained by biopsy or curettage**.

- Endometrioid carcinomas are then analysed for DNA mismatch repair deficiency
- Atypical hyperplasia cannot always be distinguished from cancer without hysterectomy; up to 40% of those diagnosed with it have carcinoma at hysterectomy

## Classify ovarian tumours.

Ovarian tumours arise from three components.
