1. **Surface epithelial-stromal tumours** (müllerian epithelium): serous, mucinous, endometrioid, clear cell and Brenner tumours [ovarian tumour with urothelial-like nests], each benign, borderline or malignant
2. **Germ cell tumours:** teratoma, dysgerminoma, yolk sac tumour, choriocarcinoma, embryonal carcinoma, polyembryoma, mixed germ cell tumours
3. **Sex cord–stromal tumours:** for example granulosa cell tumour

## What is dermoid cyst of ovary? Describe its morphology.

A **mature (benign) cystic teratoma**, called a dermoid cyst because it is almost always lined by skin-like structures; found usually in young adult females.

**Gross**
- Bilateral in 10% to 15%
- Unilocular cyst containing hair and sebaceous material
- Wall lined by opaque, grey-white, wrinkled epidermis, often with protruding hair shafts
- Teeth and areas of calcification in the wall

**Microscopy**
- Stratified squamous epithelium with sebaceous glands, hair shafts and other skin adnexal structures
- Tissues of other germ layers: cartilage, bone, thyroid and neural tissue

**Other points**
- Karyotype 46,XX
- About 1% undergo malignant transformation, most commonly to squamous cell carcinoma

## Classify germ cell tumours of ovary.

1. **Teratoma:** mature (benign, dermoid cyst); immature (malignant); monodermal or specialised (struma ovarii, carcinoid)
2. **Dysgerminoma**
3. **Yolk sac tumour** (endodermal sinus tumour)
4. **Choriocarcinoma**
5. **Embryonal carcinoma**
6. **Polyembryoma**
7. **Mixed germ cell tumours**

## Classify the surface epithelial tumours of ovary.

| Type | Benign | Borderline | Malignant |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Serous** | Cystadenoma, cystadenofibroma | Serous borderline tumour | Low- and high-grade serous adenocarcinoma |
| **Mucinous** (endocervical-like and intestinal type) | Cystadenoma, cystadenofibroma | Mucinous borderline tumour | Mucinous adenocarcinoma |
| **Endometrioid** | Cystadenoma, cystadenofibroma | Endometrioid borderline tumour | Endometrioid adenocarcinoma |
| **Clear cell** | Exceedingly rare | Exceedingly rare | Clear cell adenocarcinoma |
| **Brenner** [ovarian tumour with urothelial-like nests] | Benign Brenner tumour | Borderline Brenner tumour | Malignant Brenner tumour |

## List the tumour markers associated with ovarian tumour.

- **CA-125:** surface epithelial carcinoma; used in patients with known disease to monitor recurrence or progression; no marker is sensitive and specific enough for early diagnosis
- **Alpha-fetoprotein:** yolk sac tumour
- **Chorionic gonadotropin:** choriocarcinoma, and some dysgerminomas with syncytiotrophoblastic giant cells
- **Inhibin** (immunohistochemistry): granulosa cell tumour

## Name inflammatory lesions of breast.

Inflammatory disease of the breast is rare (less than 1% of breast symptoms) and is caused by infection, autoimmune disease, or foreign body-type reactions to keratin or secretions.

1. **Acute mastitis:** lactational, usually in the first month of breastfeeding, through cracks and fissures in the nipple; Staphylococcus aureus (abscesses) or streptococci (cellulitis)
2. **Squamous metaplasia of lactiferous ducts** (recurrent subareolar abscess, periductal mastitis, Zuska disease [recurrent periareolar abscess with squamous metaplasia of ducts]): keratinising squamous metaplasia plugs ducts; more than 90% are smokers; fistula and nipple inversion
3. **Duct ectasia:** periareolar mass with thick white nipple secretion in multiparous females in the fifth or sixth decade; not related to smoking
4. **Fat necrosis:** painless mass mimicking cancer; about half have a history of trauma or surgery
5. **Lymphocytic mastopathy** (diabetic mastopathy): hard masses with dense collagenised stroma and lymphocytic infiltrate; type 1 diabetes and Hashimoto thyroiditis [autoimmune thyroiditis causing hypothyroidism]
6. **Granulomatous mastitis:** granulomatous lobular mastitis (parous females, often idiopathic), cystic neutrophilic granulomatous mastitis (lipophilic Corynebacteria), and systemic or infective causes such as sarcoidosis, granulomatosis with polyangiitis, tuberculosis
- Inflammatory carcinoma mimics inflammation and must always be considered in an erythematous swollen breast

## What are the causes of breast lump?

Most palpable breast masses (more than 95%) are benign.

**Benign**

- **Fibroadenoma** and **cysts** (the commonest)
- Fibrocystic changes
- Fat necrosis, which may mimic cancer
- Duct ectasia
- Lactational adenoma
- Abscess from infection, during lactation
- Granulomatous mastitis

**Tumours**

- **Phyllodes tumour**
- **Invasive carcinoma**, which is usually an irregular mass
- Angiosarcoma and lymphoma (rare)

## Give the morphology of fibroadenoma of breast.

- Size varies from less than 1 cm to large tumours that replace most of the breast
- **Well-circumscribed**, rubbery, grey-white nodules that bulge above the surrounding tissue
- Often contain **slit-like spaces** lined by epithelium
- On radiography a well-circumscribed mass; the absence of adipose tissue accounts for its radiodensity
- **Microscopy:** proliferation of delicate, often myxoid **intralobular stroma**, which surrounds, pushes and distorts the epithelium; the border is sharply delimited
- Two patterns: **pericanalicular** (stroma surrounds patent ducts) and **intracanalicular** (stroma compresses ducts into cleft-like spaces)

## Classify breast tumour/carcinoma.

| Group | Types |
|---|---|
| **In situ carcinoma** | Ductal carcinoma in situ (DCIS); lobular carcinoma in situ (LCIS) |
| **Invasive carcinoma** | Invasive carcinoma of **no special type** (about 75%); **invasive lobular carcinoma** (up to 15%); special types: mucinous, tubular, cribriform, papillary, apocrine, micropapillary, metaplastic |
| **Stromal tumours** | Fibroadenoma, phyllodes tumour (intralobular stroma); angiosarcoma (interlobular stroma) |
| **Other** | Lymphoma, metastases from other sites (less than 5% of malignant tumours) |

- Paget disease of the nipple [DCIS extending into the nipple epidermis] is DCIS extending into the skin of the nipple
- Inflammatory carcinoma is not a histological type; it is a clinical presentation

## Write down the molecular classification of breast cancer.

Breast cancers fall into three major molecular groups: **luminal** (ER-positive), **HER2** and **triple negative**.

| Group | Features |
|---|---|
| **Luminal A** | ER-positive, HER2-negative; 50% to 65% of cancers are luminal; strongly ER and PR positive; well differentiated; low proliferation; gain of 1q, loss of 16q, PIK3CA mutations |
| **Luminal B** | ER-positive, HER2-negative; high proliferation; lower ER and PR expression; poorly differentiated; more aggressive than luminal A |
| **HER2-positive** | Amplification of the HER2 gene on chromosome 17q; about 20% of cancers; ER-positive or ER-negative; respond well to HER2 inhibitors |
| **Triple negative (TNBC)** | Negative for ER, PR and HER2; about 15% of cancers; basal-like gene expression; TP53 mutation; defective DNA repair, often from BRCA1 defects; high grade; relatively poor prognosis |

## What are the risk factors of breast carcinoma?

Breast cancer is multifactorial.

1. **Sex:** 99% of those affected are female
2. **Increasing age**
3. **Lifetime exposure to oestrogen:** postmenopausal hormone therapy increases the incidence of luminal cancers
4. **Genetic inheritance:**
   - 25% to 35% of cases are due to inherited susceptibility variants
   - BRCA1 and BRCA2 mutations account for 80% to 90% of single gene familial breast cancers and 3% to 6% of all breast cancers
5. **Family history:** breast cancer in a first-degree relative, in multiple relatives, or at an early age
6. **Environmental and lifestyle factors,** such as low physical activity

Factors that decrease risk are **early pregnancy** (before 20 years of age) and **prolonged breastfeeding**.

## What are the prognostic (and predictive) factors of breast cancer?

**Prognostic factors**

1. **Distant metastases:** the most important
2. **Lymph node metastases:** the most important in the absence of distant metastases; 10-year disease-free survival is 70% to 80% with no nodes involved, 35% to 40% with one to three positive nodes and 10% to 15% with more than 10 positive nodes
3. **Tumour size:** node-negative carcinoma less than 1 cm has a 10-year survival of more than 90%, falling to 77% above 2 cm
4. **Locally advanced disease:** invasion of skin or skeletal muscle
5. **Histologic grade:** high-grade tumours do worse
6. **Histologic subtype:** tubular and cribriform carcinomas have a good prognosis
7. **Lymphovascular invasion**
8. **Inflammatory carcinoma:** 3-year survival of only 3% to 10%

**Prognostic and predictive biomarkers**

- **ER** and **PR**
- **HER2**
- Gene expression profiling, which identifies slow-growing ER-positive cancers that can be spared chemotherapy

## What is the role of ER, PR and HER-2 neu in breast cancer?

- **ER:** both prognostic and predictive; ER-positive tumours (1% or more of cells with nuclear staining) respond to **endocrine therapy**, which reduces recurrence and improves survival; "low positive" tumours (1% to 10% staining) have a worse prognosis
- **PR:** ER-positive tumours are usually PR-positive; ER-positive tumours with low PR expression have worse outcomes than those with high ER and PR
- **HER2:** both prognostic and predictive; positivity indicates **aggressive behaviour** and response to **HER2-targeting agents**
- HER2 is detected by immunohistochemistry for protein overexpression or by in situ hybridisation for gene amplification
- Cancers negative for ER, PR and HER2 are **triple negative**

## Short note on Paget's disease of nipple.

**Definition and pathogenesis.** Ductal carcinoma in situ arising within the ductal system extends up the lactiferous ducts into the skin of the nipple without crossing the basement membrane. The malignant cells disrupt the squamous epithelial barrier, so fluid seeps out and forms an oozing, scaly crust.

**Features**

- Most females without a palpable mass have **DCIS** and are staged as such
- About one-half have a palpable breast mass, which usually proves to be **invasive carcinoma**

**Prognosis.** It depends on the features of the invasive carcinoma, if present, and is not affected by the presence or absence of Paget disease [disordered bone remodelling].

## 20-year-old woman with a free, movable breast lump — Probable diagnosis? Investigations to diagnose the case?

**Probable diagnosis: fibroadenoma**, the most common benign stromal tumour of the female breast.

- Most occur in females in their 20s and 30s; frequently multiple and bilateral
- Usually presents as a palpable mass in young females
- Hormonally responsive: may grow during pregnancy and regress after menopause

**Investigations**
- **Imaging:** radiograph shows a characteristically well-circumscribed mass; the absence of adipose tissue accounts for its radiodensity
- **Histology:** proliferation of intralobular stroma that surrounds, pushes and distorts the associated epithelium, with a sharply delimited border
- Gross: rubbery, white, well-circumscribed mass

## A woman has a hard, firm breast lump fixed to tissue (± skin dimpling). What is your likely diagnosis?

**Invasive breast carcinoma of no special type.**

- It most commonly presents as a **hard, irregular, radiodense mass** with a desmoplastic stromal reaction
- Malignant tumours invade across tissue planes, so they are irregular, unlike the round, rubbery, mobile benign masses
- The cut surface produces a **grating sound** from chalky-white desmoplastic stroma
- Skin dimpling, with peau d'orange, is seen in inflammatory carcinoma, where dermal lymphovascular channels are plugged by tumour cells and the skin is tethered by Cooper ligaments [suspensory ligaments of the breast]

## Same: what investigations would you perform?

1. **Mammography:** densities, architectural distortions and calcifications; the average invasive carcinoma detected is about 1 cm
2. **Ultrasonography, digital breast tomosynthesis or MRI** when the carcinoma is not visible on standard mammograms (about 10%)
3. **Histology** to confirm invasive carcinoma and grade it by the Nottingham score
4. **Biomarkers:** ER, PR and HER2
5. **Axillary lymph nodes:** histological examination, since clinical assessment is unreliable; sentinel node biopsy

## Mention four important causes of hypothyroidism.

1. **Hashimoto thyroiditis [autoimmune thyroiditis causing hypothyroidism]:** the commonest cause in iodine-sufficient areas
2. **Postablative:** surgery, radioiodine therapy or external irradiation
3. **Drugs:** lithium, amiodarone, iodides, p-aminosalicylic acid, thionamides
4. **Congenital:** endemic **iodine deficiency** (the commonest worldwide) and dyshormonogenetic goitre

## What is goitre?

**Goitre** is enlargement of the thyroid gland. It can be associated with hyperthyroidism, hypothyroidism or euthyroidism.

## What are the causes of goitre?

1. **Endemic goitre:** low iodine in soil, water and food, as in the Andes and Himalayas
2. **Sporadic goitre:**
   - Ingestion of substances that interfere with thyroid hormone synthesis
   - Hereditary enzymatic defects (**dyshormonogenetic goitre**), autosomal recessive
   - No apparent cause in most cases
3. **Multinodular goitre**
4. **Hashimoto thyroiditis** [autoimmune thyroiditis causing hypothyroidism]
5. **Graves disease** [autoimmune hyperthyroidism]

## Give the etiopathogenesis of multinodular goitre.

- **Recurrent episodes of hyperplasia and involution** of a simple goitre produce irregular enlargement
- Follicular cells **vary in their response** to external stimuli such as trophic hormones
- Cells with a growth advantage, perhaps from acquired genetic abnormalities like those in adenomas, **proliferate autonomously** and form a nodule
- **Polyclonal and monoclonal nodules** coexist in the same gland
- It occurs in sporadic and endemic forms, in older individuals, as a late complication of simple goitre

## Write in short the pathogenesis of Hashimoto's thyroiditis.

1. A breakdown in **self-tolerance** to thyroid autoantigens, shown by autoantibodies against **thyroglobulin** and **thyroid peroxidase**
2. The inciting events are unknown; possibilities are abnormal regulatory T cells or exposure of sequestered thyroid antigens
3. Strong genetic component
4. Progressive depletion of thyroid epithelial cells, replaced by lymphocytic infiltrates and **fibrosis**, by:
   - **CD8 positive cytotoxic T cell** killing of follicular cells
   - **Cytokine-mediated** damage: CD4 positive Th1 cells produce interferon gamma, which activates macrophages
   - Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity and complement-dependent damage by antithyroid antibodies, though it is unclear if the antibodies are cause or consequence

## What are the expected laboratory findings in Hashimoto's thyroiditis?

- **Autoantibodies** against thyroglobulin and thyroid peroxidase in the vast majority of patients
- As hypothyroidism develops, **T4 and T3 fall** and **TSH rises**
- **Serum TSH** is the most sensitive screening test for primary hypothyroidism
- Early on there may be transient thyrotoxicosis (**hashitoxicosis**) from release of thyroid hormones

## A 30y lady has diffuse thyroid enlargement with exophthalmos. What is your clinical diagnosis (Graves' disease)?

**Graves disease [autoimmune hyperthyroidism].**

- An autoimmune disorder with autoantibodies against the **TSH receptor**; thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulin is found in about 90%
- Diffuse hyperplasia of the thyroid, which may have an audible bruit
- Thyrotoxicosis: wide staring gaze and lid lag from sympathetic overactivity
- Infiltrative ophthalmopathy causing **exophthalmos**
- Infiltrative dermopathy (**pretibial myxoedema**) in a minority

## Same: what will be the status of thyroid hormone level?

- **Free T4 and T3 are raised**
- **TSH is depressed**
- Radioiodine scan shows diffusely increased uptake
- Thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulins and TSH-receptor antibodies are elevated

## Enumerate the tumours of thyroid.

**Benign:** thyroid (follicular) **adenoma**

**Malignant (carcinomas)**

1. **Papillary thyroid carcinoma:** about 85% of cases
2. Follicular or oncocytic thyroid carcinoma: 5% to 15%
3. High-grade differentiated and poorly differentiated carcinoma: about 5%
4. **Anaplastic (undifferentiated) carcinoma:** about 1%
5. **Medullary thyroid carcinoma:** about 5%; a neoplasm of C cells

- **NIFTP** (noninvasive follicular thyroid neoplasm with papillary-like nuclear features) is the new name for encapsulated noninvasive follicular variant papillary carcinoma

## Name the malignant tumours of thyroid.

1. **Papillary carcinoma:** about 85% of cases
2. **Follicular or oncocytic carcinoma:** 5% to 15%
3. High-grade differentiated and poorly differentiated carcinoma: about 5%
4. **Anaplastic (undifferentiated) carcinoma:** about 1%
5. **Medullary carcinoma** (C cells): about 5%

## Write a short note on papillary carcinoma of thyroid (microscopic morphology).

- **Branching papillae** with a fibrovascular stalk covered by single or multiple layers of cuboidal epithelial cells
- Nuclei with finely dispersed chromatin and an optically clear appearance: **ground-glass (Orphan Annie eye) nuclei**
- Nuclear membrane irregularities: **nuclear grooves** (coffee-bean shape) and **pseudoinclusions** (cytoplasmic invaginations)
- **Psammoma bodies**: concentrically calcified structures in papillary cores or stroma; almost never in follicular or medullary carcinomas
- Foci of **lymphatic invasion** are common; blood vessel invasion is uncommon
- Metastases to cervical lymph nodes occur in up to one-half of cases

## How will you differentiate papillary carcinoma from follicular carcinoma of thyroid?

| Feature | Papillary carcinoma | Follicular carcinoma |
|---|---|---|
| **Frequency** | About 85% | 5% to 15% |
| **Age** | Most often 25 to 50 years | Peak 40 to 60 years |
| **Iodine deficiency** | Not stated | More frequent; 25% to 40% of thyroid cancers in such areas |
| **Architecture** | Branching papillae | Small follicles with colloid |
| **Nuclei** | Ground-glass, grooves, pseudoinclusions | Lack the features of papillary carcinoma |
| **Psammoma bodies** | Present | Absent |
| **Spread** | Lymphatic; cervical nodes in up to one-half | Haematogenous, most commonly to lung |
| **Prognosis** | 10-year survival over 95% | Not stated |

## Define diabetes mellitus.

**Diabetes mellitus** is a group of metabolic disorders sharing the common feature of **hyperglycaemia**, caused by defects in insulin secretion, insulin action, or, most commonly, both. The chronic hyperglycaemia and attendant metabolic deregulation may be associated with secondary damage in multiple organ systems, especially the kidneys, eyes, nerves and blood vessels.

- The diagnostic cut-off for fasting plasma glucose is **126 mg/dL (7.0 mmol/L) or more**, confirmed on a repeat test on a separate day.

## How is diabetes mellitus classified?

1. **Type 1 diabetes:** autoimmune destruction of pancreatic β cells with absolute insulin deficiency; 5% to 10% of cases; the commonest type in patients younger than 20 years
2. **Type 2 diabetes:** peripheral resistance to insulin action with an inadequate β cell secretory response (relative insulin deficiency); 90% to 95% of cases
3. **Other types**
   - Genetic defects of β cell function: maturity-onset diabetes of the young (MODY), neonatal diabetes, maternally inherited diabetes and deafness
   - Genetic defects in insulin action
   - Exocrine pancreatic disease: chronic pancreatitis, pancreatectomy, trauma, pancreatic cancer, cystic fibrosis
   - Drugs: thiazides, nicotinic acid, phenytoin, β-adrenergic agonists
4. **Gestational diabetes**

## Mention the difference between type-I and type-II DM.

| Feature | Type 1 | Type 2 |
|---|---|---|
| **Basis** | Autoimmune β cell destruction | Insulin resistance with β cell dysfunction; no autoimmune basis |
| **Insulin** | Absolute deficiency | Relative deficiency |
| **Frequency** | 5% to 10% | 90% to 95% |
| **Age** | Commonest under 20 years | Typically obese patients over 40 years; increasingly in children |
| **Body weight** | Not stated | Most are overweight |
| **Onset** | Often abrupt; triad of polyuria, polydipsia and polyphagia | Often found on routine blood testing in asymptomatic persons |
| **Ketoacidosis** | Common | Less frequent, because higher portal vein insulin limits ketone formation |
| **Acute complication** | Diabetic ketoacidosis | Hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state |

## Give the WHO (diagnostic) criteria of diabetes mellitus.

Diabetes is diagnosed by any one of:

1. **Fasting plasma glucose** of 126 mg/dL or more
2. **Random plasma glucose** of 200 mg/dL or more, in a patient with classic hyperglycaemic signs
3. **2-hour plasma glucose** of 200 mg/dL or more during an oral glucose tolerance test with a 75 g glucose load
4. **HbA1c** of 6.5% or more

- All tests except the random glucose in a patient with classic signs must be repeated and confirmed on a separate day

## Define gestational DM.

**Gestational diabetes** is impaired glucose tolerance and diabetes developing for the first time during pregnancy in a female who was previously euglycaemic.

- Pregnancy is a diabetogenic state, because the hormonal milieu favours insulin resistance
- It typically resolves after delivery, but most affected females develop overt diabetes over the next 10 to 20 years

## What are the clinical features of a diabetic patient?

**Type 1**

1. **Polyuria, polydipsia and polyphagia**
2. Weight loss and muscle weakness despite the increased appetite
3. **Diabetic ketoacidosis** when severe

**Type 2**

1. Often diagnosed on routine blood testing in asymptomatic persons
2. Unexplained fatigue, dizziness or blurred vision

**Chronic complications** (usually 15 to 20 years after onset)

- Macrovascular: myocardial infarction, stroke, lower limb ischaemia
- Microvascular: retinopathy, nephropathy, neuropathy
- Infections

## What is OGTT? Write its procedure, indications and interpretation.

**OGTT** is the oral glucose tolerance test, in which plasma glucose is measured 2 hours after a loading dose of **75 g of glucose**.

**Interpretation**

| 2-hour plasma glucose | Meaning |
|---|---|
| 200 mg/dL or more | **Diabetes** |
| 140 to 199 mg/dL | **Impaired glucose tolerance** |

- As many as one-quarter of individuals with impaired glucose tolerance develop overt diabetes over 5 years

## What is the normal fasting blood glucose level?

- Blood glucose is normally maintained in a narrow range of **70 to 120 mg/dL**
- A fasting plasma glucose of 100 to 125 mg/dL is **impaired fasting glucose**
- A fasting plasma glucose of 126 mg/dL or more is diabetes

## What is impaired glucose tolerance (IGT)?

**Impaired glucose tolerance** is a 2-hour plasma glucose between 140 and 199 mg/dL following a 75 g oral glucose tolerance test.

- It is one of the criteria of **prediabetes**, a state of dysglycaemia that often precedes type 2 diabetes
- The others are impaired fasting glucose (fasting plasma glucose 100 to 125 mg/dL) and an HbA1c of 5.7% to 6.4%
- Individuals with prediabetes are also at significant risk of cardiovascular complications
- A fasting plasma glucose of **126 mg/dL or more**, or a 2-hour value of 200 mg/dL or more, is diabetes rather than prediabetes

## Draw different types of diabetic curves.

| Glucose tolerance curve | Fasting plasma glucose | 2-hour plasma glucose after 75 g |
|---|---|---|
| **Diabetic** | 126 mg/dL or more | 200 mg/dL or more |
| **Impaired glucose tolerance** | May be 100 to 125 mg/dL | 140 to 199 mg/dL |
| **Normal** | Within 70 to 120 mg/dL | Below 140 mg/dL |

## What is HbA1c (glycosylated haemoglobin)?

- HbA1c is haemoglobin in the red cell that has become **bound to glucose** by a nonenzymatic process during its circulating life span of about 120 days
- The amount formed is proportional to the average blood glucose
- It therefore reflects the **average blood glucose of the previous 2 to 3 months**
- A fasting sample is not needed

## Importance of HbA1c.

1. **Diagnosis of diabetes:** a level of 6.5% or more
2. **Diagnosis of prediabetes:** a level of 5.7% to 6.4%
3. **Monitoring long-term glycaemic control** in type 1 and type 2 diabetes
4. It is not affected by the daily fluctuations seen with single glucose readings
5. Used to judge the effect of treatment, usually every 3 months

## What is hyperglycaemia?

**Hyperglycaemia** is a raised blood glucose level.

- It is the common feature of all forms of diabetes mellitus
- Persistent hyperglycaemia (**glucotoxicity**) is responsible for many of the long-term complications of diabetes

## Mention the important causes of hyperglycaemia.

1. **Diabetes mellitus:** type 1, type 2, monogenic forms, gestational
2. **Acute stress:** severe infections, burns or trauma cause transient hyperglycaemia through catecholamines and cortisol, which oppose insulin
3. **Exocrine pancreatic disease:** chronic pancreatitis, pancreatectomy, pancreatic cancer, cystic fibrosis
4. **Drugs:** thiazides, nicotinic acid, phenytoin, β-adrenergic agonists
5. **Pregnancy,** which favours insulin resistance

## What is hyperglycaemia sine glycosuria? Why is sugar absent in urine?

- In diabetes the filtered glucose exceeds the **renal tubular threshold for reabsorption**, so glucose appears in the urine
- Sugar is therefore absent from the urine when the filtered glucose does not exceed the tubular threshold for reabsorption

## Write the complications of diabetes mellitus (macro- and microvascular).

**Macrovascular** (damage to large and medium-sized muscular arteries)

- Accelerated **atherosclerosis**
- Myocardial infarction, stroke and lower extremity ischaemia
- Hypertension in about 75% of those with type 2 diabetes
- Two to four times greater incidence of coronary artery disease

**Microvascular** (damage to small vessels)

- **Diabetic retinopathy:** 60% to 80% develop it; leading cause of adult blindness; neovascularisation from hypoxia-induced VEGF; also cataract and glaucoma
- **Diabetic nephropathy:** 30% to 40% develop it; first sign is moderately increased albuminuria (30 to 300 mg per day)
- **Diabetic neuropathy:** distal symmetric polyneuropathy in a glove-and-stocking pattern, autonomic neuropathy and mononeuropathy

**Other**

- Infections of the skin, tuberculosis, pneumonia and pyelonephritis
- Acute: diabetic ketoacidosis, hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state and hypoglycaemia

## What are ketone bodies (ketonuria)?

- **Ketone bodies** are **acetoacetic acid** and **β-hydroxybutyric acid**
- They are produced in the liver mitochondria by oxidation of fatty acyl coenzyme A, when insulin deficiency raises free fatty acids
- When their formation exceeds their use by peripheral tissues, **ketonaemia** and **ketonuria** result

## Write a short note on ketoacidosis / discuss the pathogenesis of DKA.

**Definition.** Diabetic ketoacidosis is a severe acute metabolic complication mainly seen in type 1 diabetes.

**Pathogenesis**

1. A precipitating factor, most often failure to take insulin, or infection, illness, trauma or drugs
2. These release **epinephrine**, which blocks residual insulin action and stimulates glucagon
3. Insulin deficiency with glucagon excess decreases peripheral glucose use and increases gluconeogenesis, so plasma glucose reaches 250 to 600 mg/dL
4. Hyperglycaemia causes **osmotic diuresis** and dehydration
5. Insulin deficiency stimulates hormone-sensitive lipase, so fat breakdown raises free fatty acids
6. In the liver these form **ketone bodies** faster than they can be used, leading to ketonaemia and ketonuria
7. Dehydration compromises urinary excretion of ketones, giving **metabolic ketoacidosis**

**Clinical features.** Fatigue, nausea and vomiting, severe abdominal pain, a characteristic **fruity odour**, and deep laboured (**Kussmaul**) breathing. Persistence leads to depressed consciousness and coma.

**Treatment.** Insulin, correction of metabolic acidosis, and treatment of the precipitating factor.

## A 48y diabetic became unconscious after chest infection with musty odour in breath. What may be the likely urine finding?

**Diabetic ketoacidosis.**

- Infection is a recognised precipitating factor
- Insulin deficiency and raised ketone bodies cause metabolic ketoacidosis, with a characteristic fruity odour of the breath
- Persistence of the ketotic state leads to depressed consciousness and coma
- Hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state is the alternative in type 2 diabetes, but it has no ketoacidosis symptoms

## A 58y obese male has weight loss, FBS 6.6, RBS 10.0 mmol/L, raised lipids. What is the diagnosis?

**Type 2 diabetes mellitus, or prediabetes progressing to it, in an obese patient; the values do not yet confirm diabetes.**

- The fasting glucose of 6.6 mmol/L and the random glucose of 10.0 mmol/L are below the diagnostic cut-offs of 126 mg/dL (7.0 mmol/L) fasting and 200 mg/dL (11.1 mmol/L) random
- They fall in the range of **impaired fasting glucose**
- Type 2 diabetes is typically seen in obese patients older than 40 years, and dyslipidaemia is a frequent accompanying risk
- Weight loss with hyperglycaemia should raise suspicion of diabetes

## Same: how will you investigate the case?

1. **Repeat fasting plasma glucose** on a separate day
2. **Oral glucose tolerance test** with 75 g glucose: 2-hour value of 200 mg/dL or more confirms diabetes; 140 to 199 mg/dL is impaired glucose tolerance
3. **HbA1c:** 6.5% or more confirms diabetes; 5.7% to 6.4% is prediabetes
4. Test for complications: urinary albumin (first sign of nephropathy), eye examination for retinopathy, assessment of neuropathy

## Write a short note on basal cell carcinoma.

**Definition.** **Basal cell carcinoma** is a distinctive locally aggressive cutaneous tumour associated with mutations that activate the **Hedgehog signalling pathway**. It is the most common invasive cancer in humans. It is slow growing and rarely metastasises.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Occurs at **sun-exposed sites** in lightly pigmented older adults
- Incidence is increased in **immunosuppression** and in disorders of DNA repair such as **xeroderma pigmentosum**
- **Nevoid basal cell carcinoma syndrome** (Gorlin syndrome [basal cell naevus syndrome from PTCH1 mutation], basal cell naevus syndrome): autosomal dominant; multiple basal cell carcinomas often before age 20 years, with medulloblastomas, ovarian fibromas, odontogenic keratocysts and pits of the palms and soles
- Gene is **PTCH** (9q22), a tumour-suppressor gene: germline loss-of-function mutation in one allele, with the second allele inactivated in tumours
- PTCH is the receptor for sonic hedgehog (SHH); it normally blocks SMO. Loss of PTCH function causes constitutive activation of **SMO** and the transcription factor **GLI1**
- Hedgehog-activating mutations are also common in sporadic tumours; about one-third of PTCH mutations are C to T transitions, the hallmark of UV damage

**Morphology**

- Pearly papules with prominent dilated subepidermal blood vessels (**telangiectasias**); these may be less prominent in darker skin
- Some tumours contain melanin and resemble naevi or melanomas
- Advanced lesions may ulcerate, and extensive local invasion of bone or facial sinuses may follow years of neglect, hence the name **rodent ulcers**
- **Superficial basal cell carcinoma** is an erythematous, occasionally pigmented plaque that may mimic melanoma
- Tumour cells resemble the basal cell layer of the epidermis; they arise from the epidermis or follicular epithelium and do not occur on mucosal surfaces
- Two patterns: **multifocal superficial** growth from the epidermis, and **nodular** growth into the dermis as cords and islands of basophilic cells with hyperchromatic nuclei in a mucinous matrix
- **Palisading** of the peripheral cells of the islands, with **retraction clefts** between tumour and stroma; the clefts help to separate it from appendage tumours such as trichoepithelioma

**Clinical features and complications**

- Metastasis is very rare; fewer than 0.5% are locally aggressive and disfiguring
- Most are cured by local excision
- Locally advanced or metastatic disease often responds to **Hedgehog pathway inhibitors** or immune checkpoint inhibitors

## Write a short note on nevus.

**Definition.** **Melanocytic naevi** (moles) are common benign neoplasms strongly associated with acquired activating mutations in components of the RAS signalling pathway. Acquired melanocytic naevi are the most common type and are found in virtually all individuals.

**Pathogenesis**

- Many have acquired mutations causing constitutive activation of **RAS** or of **BRAF**, which lies immediately downstream of RAS
- Naevi rarely give rise to melanoma because of **oncogene-induced senescence**: activated RAS or BRAF causes only limited proliferation, followed by permanent growth arrest through accumulation of **p16**, an inhibitor of CDK4 and CDK6
- This protective response is disrupted in melanoma

**Morphology**

- Common acquired naevi are tan to brown, uniformly pigmented, small (usually less than 6 mm), flat macules or elevated papules with well-defined, rounded borders
- **Junctional naevus**: the earliest lesion; nests of round cells along the dermoepidermal junction, with uniform rounded nuclei, inconspicuous nucleoli and little or no mitotic activity
- **Compound naevus**: growth into the dermis as nests or cords of cells, so epidermal and dermal components are both present; raised and dome shaped
- **Intradermal naevus**: in older lesions the epidermal nests are lost entirely
- Superficial naevus cells are larger, produce melanin and grow in nests; deeper cells are smaller, produce little or no pigment and appear as cords and single cells
- At the deepest extent the cells become fusiform and grow in fascicles resembling neural tissue (**neurotisation**), with progressive loss of tyrosinase activity; this helps to distinguish naevi from melanomas

**Variants**

| Variant | Features | Clinical significance |
|---|---|---|
| **Congenital naevus** | Deep dermal and sometimes subcutaneous growth around adnexa, neurovascular bundles and vessel walls | Present at birth; large variants have increased melanoma risk |
| **Blue naevus** | Non-nested dermal infiltration; highly dendritic, heavily pigmented cells | Black-blue nodule; often confused with melanoma clinically |
| **Spitz naevus** (spindle and epithelioid cell naevus) | Fascicular growth; large plump cells with pink-blue cytoplasm | Common in children; red-pink nodule; often confused with haemangioma |
| **Halo naevus** | Lymphocytic infiltration around naevus cells | Host immune response against naevus cells and surrounding normal melanocytes |
| **Dysplastic naevus** | Coalescent intraepidermal nests; cytological atypia | Potential marker or precursor of melanoma |

**Clinical features and complications**

- May become more prominent during pregnancy, indicating hormone sensitivity
- **Dysplastic naevi** are larger than most acquired naevi (often more than 5 mm), with variable pigmentation and irregular borders, and occur on sun-exposed and protected surfaces
- Dysplastic naevi are best regarded as markers of increased melanoma risk; most never progress
- In **dysplastic naevus syndrome** (autosomal dominant), the probability of melanoma is over 50% by age 60 years

## A 70y male has a black ulcerated lesion of right great toe for 6 months, spreading rapidly. What is the differential diagnosis?

**Malignant melanoma** is the most likely diagnosis: it shows striking variation in colour, with shades of black, brown, red, dark blue and grey, irregular and often notched borders, and a vertical growth phase heralded by a nodule.

The lesions that it must be distinguished from are:

- **Acral lentiginous melanoma**: the melanoma type that arises on non-sun-exposed sites
- **Pigmented basal cell carcinoma**: some tumours contain melanin and superficially resemble naevi or melanomas; advanced lesions ulcerate
- **Blue naevus**: a black-blue nodule often confused with melanoma clinically
- **Seborrhoeic keratosis**: a pigmented lesion; small pore-like keratin-filled openings seen with a hand lens help to tell it from melanoma

## Same: how will you confirm the disease?

**Excision biopsy with histological examination.**

- Cells are larger than normal melanocytes, with enlarged irregular nuclei, chromatin clumped at the nuclear membrane and prominent eosinophilic nucleoli
- **Neurotisation** (maturation) is absent in the deep invasive part, unlike a naevus
- Atypical lesions in the histological grey zone need complete excision and close follow-up

The prognosis is assessed from:

1. **Breslow thickness** (depth of invasion)
2. Number of mitoses
3. Tumour regression
4. Ulceration of the overlying skin
5. Tumour-infiltrating lymphocytes
6. Site (central body or extremity)

- **Sentinel lymph node biopsy**: most melanomas first metastasise to regional nodes; micrometastasis, which can be shown with the melanocytic marker HMB-45, confers a worse prognosis
- Metastatic melanomas are tested for **BRAF** mutation

## What is osteomyelitis?

**Osteomyelitis** is bone and marrow inflammation, virtually always secondary to infection.

## Name the different types of osteomyelitis.

Organisms of all types can cause it, including viruses, parasites, fungi and bacteria, but certain pyogenic bacteria and mycobacteria are the most common.

1. **Pyogenic osteomyelitis**, almost always bacterial
2. **Mycobacterial osteomyelitis**, including tuberculous osteomyelitis of the spine (**Pott disease** [tuberculosis of the spine])
3. **Skeletal syphilis**, congenital or acquired

By stage, the changes are acute, subacute and chronic.

## What are its predisposing factors, etiologic agents and causes?

**Routes of infection**

1. Haematogenous spread
2. Extension from a contiguous site
3. Direct implantation after compound fracture or orthopaedic procedures

**Predisposing factors**

- In otherwise healthy children, most osteomyelitis is haematogenous, from trivial mucosal injuries or minor skin infections
- In adults: open fractures, surgical procedures and **diabetes** (infections of the feet)
- **Sickle cell anaemia**: osteonecrosis acts as a nidus, and loss of splenic function impairs immunity
- Infants: epiphyseal infection can spread to the joint
- Delayed diagnosis, extensive bone necrosis, inadequate antibiotic treatment or debridement, and weakened host immunity favour failure to resolve

**Aetiological agents**

| Setting | Organism |
|---|---|
| Most culture-positive pyogenic cases | **Staphylococcus aureus**, 80% to 90% |
| Neonates | Group B streptococci and **E. coli** |
| Older children | Gram-positive organisms such as **S. aureus** |
| Direct spread, surgery or open fractures | Mixed bacterial infections |
| Sickle cell anaemia | **Salmonella** and other gram-negative organisms |
| Mycobacterial osteomyelitis | **Mycobacterium tuberculosis** |
| Rare | **Treponema pallidum** and yaws |

- Specific organisms are identified in only about 50% of patients

## Give the morphology of pyogenic osteomyelitis.

- The changes depend on the stage (acute, subacute and chronic) and the site of infection
- In neonates the metaphysis, epiphysis or both are infected; in older children the metaphysis; in adults after growth plate closure the epiphysis and subchondral regions

**Acute phase**

1. Bacteria proliferate and induce a **neutrophilic reaction**
2. **Necrosis** of bone cells and marrow within 48 hours
3. Spread through the **Haversian canals** to the periosteum
4. **Subperiosteal abscesses** dissect along the bone surface in children, because the periosteum is loosely attached; lifting of the periosteum further impairs blood supply
5. Soft tissue abscesses after periosteal rupture, which may drain through **sinuses** to the skin

**Subacute and chronic phase**

- During the first week, chronic inflammatory cells release cytokines that stimulate bone resorption, **fibrous tissue ingrowth** and deposition of **reactive bone** at the periphery
- Dead bone is the **sequestrum**
- Reactive bone forms a living shell, the **involucrum**, around the dead infected bone
- Histology: marrow fibrosis, sequestrum, and a chronic inflammatory infiltrate of lymphocytes and plasma cells

## Write the pathogenesis (aetiopathogenesis) of (chronic) osteomyelitis.

- Staphylococcal cell wall proteins bind to bone matrix components such as collagen, which helps bacteria to adhere to bone
- Bacteria proliferate and induce a neutrophilic reaction, and bone cells and marrow become necrotic
- Chronic inflammatory cells release cytokines that stimulate bone resorption, fibrous tissue ingrowth and reactive bone deposition
- Dead bone persists as **sequestrum** within an **involucrum** of new bone
- In 5% to 25% of cases acute osteomyelitis fails to resolve, which leads to chronic infection; this is likely with:
  1. delayed diagnosis
  2. extensive bone necrosis
  3. inadequate antibiotic treatment or surgical debridement
  4. weakened host immunity
- Chronic infection may flare spontaneously after years of dormancy

## Write a short note on chronic osteomyelitis.

**Definition.** Bone and marrow inflammation, virtually always secondary to infection, that has failed to resolve; 5% to 25% of acute osteomyelitis does not resolve.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Delayed diagnosis, extensive bone necrosis, inadequate antibiotic treatment or surgical debridement, and weakened host immunity
- Chronic inflammatory cells release cytokines that stimulate bone resorption, fibrous tissue ingrowth and reactive bone formation

**Morphology**

- **Sequestrum** (dead bone) surrounded by **involucrum** (shell of living new bone); a drainage tract in the involucrum reveals the necrotic cortex
- Marrow fibrosis, sequestrum, and a chronic inflammatory infiltrate of lymphocytes and plasma cells

**Clinical features and complications**

- Course punctuated by spontaneous flares after years of dormancy
- Complications: pathological fracture, secondary (reactive) **amyloidosis**, endocarditis, **sepsis**, **squamous cell carcinoma** in draining sinus tracts, and sarcoma in the infected bone

## Write the complications of (chronic) osteomyelitis.

1. Pathological fracture
2. Secondary (reactive) **amyloidosis**
3. **Endocarditis**
4. **Sepsis**
5. **Squamous cell carcinoma** in draining sinus tracts
6. **Sarcoma** in the infected bone
7. Spontaneous flares after years of dormancy

- In infants, epiphyseal infection can spread to a joint and cause septic (suppurative) arthritis, articular cartilage destruction and permanent disability
- Soft tissue abscesses may channel to the skin as draining sinuses

## A 16y boy has severe left tibial pain, high fever, TLC 18000, periosteal thickening on X-ray. What is your clinical diagnosis?

**Acute (haematogenous) pyogenic osteomyelitis of the tibia.**

- In otherwise healthy children, most osteomyelitis is haematogenous and more common in long bones
- It presents acutely as a systemic illness with malaise, fever, chills, **leucocytosis** and intense throbbing pain over the affected region
- The commonest organism is **Staphylococcus aureus**, responsible for 80% to 90% of culture-positive cases
- Infection lifts the periosteum, and reactive bone is deposited at the periphery of the infection

**Differential diagnosis:** **Ewing sarcoma** [small round blue cell bone tumour with t(11;22)] also presents in children with a painful, tender, warm, swollen mass and systemic findings that mimic infection (fever, raised sedimentation rate, anaemia, leucocytosis), with a periosteal reaction.

## Same: how would you confirm it?

- **Plain radiograph**: a lytic focus of bone destruction with associated reactive bone
- **MRI**: more sensitive and specific for the changes of osteomyelitis
- **Biopsy and microbial culture**: required to identify the pathogen in most instances; the organism is identified in only about 50% of patients
- Histology of the biopsy: neutrophilic reaction and necrosis of bone and marrow in the acute phase

## Write a short note on Pott's disease.

**Definition.** **Pott disease** [tuberculosis of the spine] is mycobacterial (tuberculous) osteomyelitis of the spine. The spine is involved in 40% of mycobacterial osteomyelitis cases.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Usually bloodborne, from a focus of active visceral disease elsewhere
- Direct extension may occur, for example from tracheobronchial nodes into adjacent vertebrae
- About 1% to 3% of individuals with tuberculosis develop osseous infection

**Morphology**

- **Caseating granulomas**, as in other sites
- Infection tracks through the intervertebral discs to involve multiple vertebrae and the surrounding soft tissues
- More destructive and resistant to control than pyogenic osteomyelitis

**Clinical features and complications**

- Localised pain, low-grade fever, chills or weight loss
- Disc and vertebral destruction lead to permanent compression fractures, which produce **scoliosis**, **kyphosis** and neurological deficits from compression of the spinal cord and nerves
- Sinus tract formation, abscesses, tuberculous arthritis and **amyloidosis**

## Name the bone-forming tumours / classify bone tumours.

Bone tumours are classified according to the normal cell type they recapitulate or the matrix they produce. Lesions without a normal tissue counterpart are grouped by clinicopathological features.

| Category | Benign | Malignant |
|---|---|---|
| **Bone forming** | **Osteoid osteoma**, osteoblastoma | **Osteosarcoma** |
| **Cartilage forming** | Osteochondroma, chondroma, chondroblastoma, chondromyxoid fibroma | Chondrosarcoma |
| **Unknown origin** | **Giant cell tumour**, aneurysmal bone cyst | **Ewing sarcoma** [small round blue cell bone tumour with t(11;22)], adamantinoma |
| **Notochordal** | None | Chordoma |

## Write in brief about the morphology of osteosarcoma.

- Large, gritty, grey-white tumours that often contain haemorrhage and cystic degeneration
- Frequently destroy the surrounding cortices to produce a soft tissue mass, spread extensively in the medullary canal and replace the haematopoietic marrow
- Infrequently penetrate the epiphyseal plate or enter the joint
- Pleomorphism, large hyperchromatic nuclei, bizarre tumour giant cells and abundant mitoses, including abnormal (tripolar) forms
- Extensive necrosis and intravascular invasion are common
- Diagnosis requires malignant cells producing **unmineralised osteoid** or **mineralised bone**, typically fine and lacelike, but sometimes broad sheets or primitive trabeculae
- If the cells also produce abundant cartilage, the tumour is a **chondroblastic osteosarcoma**
- Radiologically: destructive mixed lytic and sclerotic mass; lifting of the periosteum produces **Codman triangle** [periosteal elevation in aggressive bone tumours]

## Write a short note on Ewing's sarcoma.

**Definition.** **Ewing sarcoma** [small round blue cell bone tumour with t(11;22)] is a malignant bone tumour strongly associated with oncogenic fusion genes composed of portions of the **EWSR1** gene and genes encoding ETS family transcription factors. It accounts for about 10% of primary malignant bone tumours and is the second most common bone sarcoma in children after osteosarcoma.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- More than 90% contain a balanced translocation involving **EWSR1** on chromosome 22 and **FLI1** on chromosome 11
- The fusion gene encodes a chimeric **EWSR1/FLI1** protein that binds chromatin and dysregulates transcription
- Fewer than 10% have fusion genes involving other members of the FET or ETS gene families
- The cell of origin is not certain: mesenchymal stem cells and primitive neuroectodermal cells are possible sources

**Morphology**

- Arises in the medullary cavity and invades the cortex, periosteum and soft tissue
- Grossly soft, tan-white, with areas of haemorrhage and necrosis
- Sheets of uniform **small round cells**, more cohesive than lymphocytes, with scant cytoplasm that may be clear because of glycogen
- **Homer-Wright rosettes** (circular groups of cells with a central fibrillary core) may be present
- Tumour cells do not produce bone or cartilage

**Clinical features and complications**

- About 80% of patients are younger than 20 years; boys are affected slightly more often
- Usually in the **diaphysis** of long bones, especially the femur, and the flat bones of the pelvis; about 20% are extraskeletal
- Painful, enlarging mass; the site is tender, warm and swollen, with systemic findings that mimic infection: fever, raised red cell sedimentation rate, anaemia and leucocytosis
- Radiograph: destructive lytic tumour with permeative or moth-eaten margins and an **onion-skin** periosteal reaction
- With neoadjuvant chemotherapy, surgery and adjuvant chemotherapy, 5-year survival is 75% and long-term cure 50%; chemotherapy-induced necrosis is a positive prognostic indicator

## Write a short note on giant cell tumour of bone.

**Definition.** **Giant cell tumour** is a neoplasm composed of monotonous mononuclear stromal cells, macrophages and osteoclast-type giant cells. While benign, it can be locally aggressive.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Most cells are non-neoplastic osteoclasts and their precursors
- The neoplastic cells are primitive osteoblast precursors that express high levels of **RANKL**, which promotes proliferation of osteoclast precursors and their differentiation into mature osteoclasts
- Absent feedback between osteoblasts and osteoclasts leads to localised but highly destructive bone resorption
- The neoplastic cells have mutations in the **H3-3A** gene encoding histone 3.3

**Morphology**

- Large, red-brown masses that frequently undergo cystic degeneration
- Often destroy the overlying cortex, giving a bulging soft tissue mass delineated by a thin shell of reactive bone
- Uniform oval mononuclear tumour cells and abundant osteoclast-type giant cells with 100 or more nuclei; necrosis and mitotic activity may be prominent

**Clinical features and complications**

- Usually arises in the third to fifth decades, in the **epiphysis** of long bones, and may extend into the metaphysis; most are near the knee, in the distal femur or proximal tibia
- Arthritis-like symptoms, because it arises near joints; pathological fracture
- Treated by curettage; 40% to 60% recur locally
- Up to 4% metastasise to the lungs, but these regress spontaneously or can be cured by excision
- The RANKL inhibitor **denosumab** is an alternative to surgery when resection would cause significant morbidity

## Name 2 systemic diseases that affect joints.

1. **Rheumatoid arthritis**: a chronic autoimmune disorder that principally attacks the joints, producing a non-suppurative, proliferative, inflammatory synovitis; extra-articular lesions may also occur
2. **Gout**: transient attacks of acute arthritis initiated by monosodium urate crystals deposited within and around joints, with excessive uric acid in the tissues and body fluids

## What is meningitis? What are the types?

**Definition.** **Meningitis** is an inflammatory process involving the leptomeninges and CSF within the subarachnoid space, usually caused by infection. **Meningoencephalitis** is inflammation of the meninges and brain parenchyma, most commonly viral.

**Types** (by aetiology and clinical evolution of infectious meningitis)

1. **Acute pyogenic** meningitis, usually bacterial
2. **Aseptic** meningitis, usually acute or subacute viral
3. **Chronic** meningitis, usually tuberculous, spirochaetal or cryptococcal

- **Chemical meningitis** may follow an irritant introduced into the subarachnoid space
- Meningitis may also occur in a systemic autoimmune disease

## What are the causes of neonatal meningitis?

- **Escherichia coli**
- **Group B streptococci**

## Mention the CSF findings of different types of meningitis.

| Type | Cells | Protein | Glucose | Other |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Acute pyogenic** | Numerous neutrophils | Increased | Markedly reduced | Cloudy or frankly purulent; increased pressure |
| **Aseptic (viral)** | Lymphocytic pleocytosis | Only moderately increased | Nearly always normal | Clear; normal or mildly increased pressure |
| **Tuberculous** | Mononuclear cells, or a mixture of neutrophils and mononuclear cells | Elevated, often strikingly | Moderately reduced or normal | Cloudy or yellow; increased pressure |
| **Chemical** | Neutrophilic pleocytosis | Increased | Usually normal | Sterile CSF |

## Give the differences in CSF findings between pyogenic, tubercular and viral meningitis.

| Feature | Pyogenic meningitis | Tuberculous meningitis | Viral meningitis |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Appearance** | Cloudy or frankly purulent | Cloudy or yellow | Clear |
| **Pressure** | Increased | Increased | Normal or mildly increased |
| **Cells** | Numerous neutrophils | Mononuclear cells, or neutrophils with mononuclear cells | Lymphocytic pleocytosis |
| **Protein** | Increased | Elevated, often strikingly | Only moderately increased |
| **Glucose** | Markedly reduced | Moderately reduced or normal | Nearly always normal |

## Why is glucose reduced in pyogenic meningitis?

- Markedly reduced CSF glucose (hypoglycorrhachia) is a characteristic finding of acute pyogenic meningitis
- It results mainly from increased **anaerobic glycolysis** in the subarachnoid space, induced by TNF-alpha and leucocytes
- The cause is still debated: the leucocyte count does not fully explain it, and the CSF protein concentration correlates more strongly with the low glucose than the cell count does

## Why is CSF appearance turbid in pyogenic meningitis?

- In pyogenic meningitis, **neutrophils** fill the subarachnoid space and a suppurative exudate covers the brain surface
- A lumbar puncture therefore yields **cloudy or frankly purulent** CSF with numerous neutrophils and increased protein
- Normal CSF is clear

## A patient has high fever, neck rigidity/pain, ± purpuric rash, leukocytosis. What is your clinical diagnosis?

**Acute pyogenic (bacterial) meningitis.**

- Systemic signs of infection with symptoms of meningeal irritation: headache, photophobia, irritability, clouding of consciousness and **neck stiffness**
- Neisseria meningitidis is the cause in adolescents and young adults; clusters of cases raise public health concerns
- Complication: **Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome** [adrenal haemorrhage in meningococcal septicaemia] (septicaemia with haemorrhagic infarction of the adrenal glands), most often with meningococcal and pneumococcal meningitis

## Same: what investigations/CSF findings will confirm it?

**Lumbar puncture and CSF examination.**

- **Cloudy or frankly purulent** CSF
- Numerous **neutrophils**
- Increased CSF pressure
- Increased protein
- Markedly reduced glucose
- **Gram stain** shows variable numbers of bacteria, particularly in untreated meningitis, and culture identifies the organism

## What is pleocytosis?

- Pleocytosis is an **increased cell count**, particularly an increased white blood cell count, in a body fluid such as **cerebrospinal fluid**
- In CSF it means more than 5 white cells per microlitre
- **Lymphocytic pleocytosis**: aseptic meningitis
- **Neutrophilic pleocytosis**: chemical meningitis

## Write down the indications of CSF examination.

CSF analysis is indicated in:

1. **Suspected CNS infection** (meningitis, encephalitis, myelitis, radiculitis and neuritis): the most common indication and a medical emergency; it identifies the organism and guides antimicrobial treatment
2. **Subarachnoid haemorrhage**: when CT is delayed or equivocal, because bleeding may not be seen on imaging
3. **Inflammatory and demyelinating disease**: multiple sclerosis and Guillain-Barré syndrome [acute demyelinating polyneuropathy]
4. **Malignancy**: spread of malignant disease, such as lymphoma or leptomeningeal carcinomatosis, into the CSF space
5. Other neurological conditions: spontaneous intracranial hypotension and idiopathic intracranial hypertension

## Write a short note on peculiarities of CNS neoplasm.

The following make CNS tumours different from tumours elsewhere:

1. The clinical course is strongly influenced by the **pattern of growth and location**, as much as by histological grade
2. Even **benign tumours and low-grade malignancies** can cause serious deficits and may be fatal; a benign posterior fossa meningioma can cause cardiorespiratory arrest by compressing vital centres in the medulla
3. **Diffuse gliomas** infiltrate the brain widely, so complete surgical resection is not possible without compromising neurological function
4. Even the most highly malignant gliomas **rarely metastasise outside the CNS**
5. Some paediatric tumours, such as **medulloblastoma**, spread through the CSF in the subarachnoid space and disseminate far from the original site
6. Grading uses the 2021 WHO classification, which gives CNS WHO grade 1 to grade 4 by biological behaviour and increasingly includes molecular and genetic criteria
7. Malignant tumours tend to become more aggressive with time because of clonal evolution, shown as a higher grade
8. Seventy percent of childhood CNS tumours arise in the posterior fossa; a comparable number in adults arise in the cerebral hemispheres above the tentorium

## Write a short note on meningioma.

**Definition.** **Meningiomas** are predominantly benign tumours of adults that arise from the meningothelial cells of the arachnoid and are usually attached to the dura. They are the most common primary brain tumours.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Prior radiation therapy to the head and neck, typically decades earlier, is a risk factor
- Loss of chromosome 22, especially 22q12, which carries the **NF2** gene (merlin); 50% to 60% of sporadic meningiomas have NF2 mutations
- Without NF2 mutations: TRAF7, KLF4, AKT1, SMO and PIK3CA mutations, mostly in skull base tumours with lower risk of progression
- Higher grade: multiple chromosomal losses, **TERT** promoter mutations and homozygous **CDKN2A** deletions; the last two define CNS WHO grade 3

**Morphology**

- Rubbery, rounded or bosselated dural masses that compress the underlying brain but are easily separated from it; may grow en plaque, with hyperostosis of adjacent bone
- Firm to finely gritty because of **psammomatous calcifications**
- Most are CNS WHO grade 1; patterns are meningothelial, fibroblastic, transitional (with **whorls**) and psammomatous (with **psammoma bodies**, concentric rings of calcification)
- **Atypical** (grade 2) are about one-quarter, with increased mitoses or brain invasion; **anaplastic** (grade 3) are 1% to 3%

**Clinical features and complications**

- Slow growing; vague non-localising symptoms or focal findings from compression of the brain
- Sites: parasagittal convexity, lateral convexity dura, sphenoid wing, olfactory groove, sella turcica and foramen magnum
- Uncommon in children; 2 to 1 female predominance in adults, 10 to 1 for spinal meningiomas
- Multiple lesions with vestibular schwannoma or spinal ependymoma suggest **NF2**
- Progesterone receptors are often expressed; growth may be faster in pregnancy and regress after delivery
- Higher grades recur more and may need radiation therapy in addition to surgery

## Give the normal serum levels of Na⁺, K⁺, Cl⁻ and HCO₃⁻.

| Electrolyte | Normal serum level |
|---|---|
| **Sodium** (Na⁺) | 136 to 146 mmol/L |
| **Potassium** (K⁺) | 3.5 to 5.0 mmol/L |
| **Chloride** (Cl⁻) | 95 to 105 mmol/L |
| **Bicarbonate** (HCO₃⁻) | 22 to 28 mmol/L |

## What is the normal pH of blood and urine?

| Fluid | Normal pH |
|---|---|
| **Blood** | 7.35 to 7.45 |
| **Urine** | 4.5 to 8.0 |

- Blood pH below 7.35 is **acidaemia** and above 7.45 is **alkalaemia**
- Normal urine is slightly acidic; it varies with diet and with acid-base disturbances

## What do you mean by metabolic acidosis?

**Metabolic acidosis** is an acid-base disturbance with an increase in hydrogen ion concentration in the systemic circulation, shown as a **fall in serum bicarbonate** (below 24 mEq/L) and a **blood pH below 7.35**.

- It signifies an underlying disorder that needs correction
- The respiratory system compensates by hyperventilation, which lowers the arterial PCO2; compensation never fully corrects the pH

## What are the causes of metabolic acidosis?

**Mechanisms:** increased production of acid, decreased excretion of acid, acid ingestion, and renal or gastrointestinal loss of bicarbonate.

The causes are classified by the **anion gap** (sodium minus the sum of chloride and bicarbonate; normal about 12):

| Type | Causes |
|---|---|
| **High anion gap** | Lactic acidosis from anaerobic metabolism; diabetic ketoacidosis; **uraemia** (renal failure); salicylates; methanol and ethylene glycol; paraldehyde; iron and isoniazid; cyanide and carbon monoxide; arsenic; toluene; metformin |
| **Normal anion gap** (hyperchloraemic) | **Diarrhoea** (gastrointestinal loss of bicarbonate); **renal tubular acidosis**; Addison disease [primary adrenocortical insufficiency]; ureterosigmoid or pancreatic fistulae; acetazolamide; total parenteral nutrition; large volumes of normal saline |

- A positive urine anion gap points to renal loss of bicarbonate such as renal tubular acidosis; a negative value points to non-renal loss such as diarrhoea

## What conditions may lead to metabolic alkalosis? Give the biochemical changes.

**Conditions**

1. **Loss of gastric acid**: prolonged vomiting or nasogastric suction
2. **Diuretics**: thiazide and loop diuretics
3. **Volume depletion** (hypovolaemia) and chloride depletion
4. **Hypokalaemia**, which shifts hydrogen ions into cells and increases renal bicarbonate reabsorption
5. **Mineralocorticoid excess**: Conn syndrome [primary hyperaldosteronism], Cushing syndrome [glucocorticoid excess], Bartter [inherited loop of Henle salt wasting causing hypokalaemic alkalosis] and Gitelman [inherited distal tubular salt wasting causing hypokalaemic alkalosis] syndromes, Liddle syndrome [inherited renal sodium-channel overactivity causing hypertension with hypokalaemia]
6. Alkali (bicarbonate) administration
7. Recovery after prolonged hypercapnia

**Biochemical changes**

- Primary **increase in serum bicarbonate**
- Raised blood pH, though it may be nearly normal
- **Hypokalaemia** and **hypochloraemia**
- Compensatory hypoventilation, with a rise in arterial PCO2
- Urine chloride below 10 mEq/L in chloride-responsive causes (vomiting, diuretics) and above 20 mEq/L in chloride-resistant causes

## Write a short note on respiratory acidosis.

**Definition.** **Respiratory acidosis** is an acid-base disturbance due to alveolar hypoventilation, with a primary rise in arterial PCO2, a fall in the bicarbonate to PCO2 ratio and a **fall in blood pH**.

**Causes**

- Impaired respiratory drive from drugs or CNS disease
- Airflow obstruction: chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), asthma, sleep apnoea, airway oedema
- Obesity hypoventilation syndrome, amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and severe thoracic skeletal defects

**Biochemical changes**

- Arterial blood gas: **PCO2 above 45 mmHg**, **pH below 7.35**
- **Acute**: bicarbonate rises by 1 mEq/L for each 10 mmHg rise in PCO2
- **Chronic**: renal compensation over days; bicarbonate rises by 4 mEq/L for each 10 mmHg rise in PCO2 and exceeds 30 mEq/L; pH is normal or near normal

**Clinical features**

- Acute: headache, confusion, drowsiness; with severe prolonged hypoventilation, altered mental status, myoclonus and seizures
- Chronic form is often asymptomatic

## A 10y boy has severe diarrhoea. What changes do you expect in his electrolyte and acid-base status?

**Normal anion gap (hyperchloraemic) metabolic acidosis.**

- Diarrhoea causes loss of **bicarbonate** from the gut, so serum bicarbonate and blood pH fall
- Chloride rises to balance the fall in bicarbonate, so the anion gap stays normal
- The urine anion gap is negative, since the loss is non-renal
- Hypokalaemia accompanies the acidosis of diarrhoea
- The respiratory compensation is hyperventilation, which lowers the arterial PCO2

## A 25y man in shock after RTA passed 40 ml urine in 24 h. What will be his acid-base and electrolyte status?

**High anion gap metabolic acidosis.**

- Oliguria of 40 mL in 24 hours after shock indicates **acute kidney injury**, so acid excretion falls (**uraemia**)
- Shock causes anaerobic metabolism and **lactic acid** accumulation
- Unmeasured anions increase, so the anion gap is **raised**; serum bicarbonate and blood pH are low
- The respiratory compensation is hyperventilation, which lowers the arterial PCO2

## Write the clinical importance of serum calcium.

**Normal total serum calcium** is 2.12 to 2.62 mmol/L (8.5 to 10.5 mg/dL); **ionised calcium** is 1.16 to 1.31 mmol/L. About 40% is protein-bound (mainly to albumin and globulin), about 9% is chelated, and about 51% is free (ionised) and physiologically active. Over 99% of body calcium is in bone as hydroxyapatite. Total calcium is corrected for albumin by adding about 0.8 mg/dL for each 1 g/dL that albumin falls below 4 g/dL.

**Hypercalcaemia**

- **Causes**: PTH-mediated (hyperparathyroidism); non-PTH-mediated: **malignancy** (the most common cause of rapidly progressive hypercalcaemia), granulomatous disorders, drugs, endocrinopathies and genetic causes
- **Clinical features**: mild (below 11.5 mg/dL) is usually asymptomatic; above 11.5 mg/dL there is nausea, vomiting, confusion, headache, abdominal or flank pain, constipation, depression, weakness, myalgia, arthralgia, polyuria, polydipsia and nocturia; coma in severe cases
- **Signs**: hypertension, bradycardia, hyperreflexia, tongue fasciculations
- **ECG**: shortened QT interval, flattened or inverted T waves, J waves, prolonged PR and QRS intervals

**Hypocalcaemia**

- **Causes**: **hypoparathyroidism** (most often after thyroid, parathyroid or neck surgery; about 75% of cases), hungry bone syndrome after parathyroidectomy, **vitamin D deficiency**, chronic kidney disease, pseudohypoparathyroidism, hypomagnesaemia, acute pancreatitis, sepsis, massive blood transfusion (citrate binds calcium), drugs (bisphosphonates, denosumab, cinacalcet, cisplatin, foscarnet)
- **Clinical features**: **tetany**, paraesthesia (perioral and distal), seizures, laryngospasm, QT prolongation with risk of arrhythmia, heart failure
- **Signs**: **Chvostek sign** [facial twitching on tapping the facial nerve in hypocalcaemia] (facial twitch on tapping the facial nerve) and **Trousseau sign** [migratory thrombophlebitis associated with cancer] (carpopedal spasm on inflating a cuff)
- **Chronic**: dry skin, coarse hair, brittle nails, poor dentition, cataracts

**Approach**

- Hypocalcaemia: check albumin first to exclude factitious hypocalcaemia, then PTH, magnesium, vitamin D, phosphate, renal and liver function, and an ECG
- Hypercalcaemia: confirm with albumin and ionised calcium, then PTH, renal function, TSH and electrolytes; if malignancy is suspected, look for lung, breast or kidney masses and for myeloma, lymphoma or leukaemia

## What are the indications of enzymes of clinical importance?

Serum enzymes are measured:

1. To **detect tissue damage**, because intracellular enzymes leak into the blood from damaged cells
2. To **localise the site** of damage, showing which parts of the body are involved
3. To **quantify the extent** of damage
4. For diagnosis, differential diagnosis, prognosis and early detection, for example alanine aminotransferase (ALT) rises in viral hepatitis before jaundice appears
5. To follow the **course of liver disease** and to check the hepatic origin of a raised alkaline phosphatase
6. To evaluate pancreatic and salivary gland disease
7. To monitor **organophosphate poisoning** and hepatic synthetic capacity (cholinesterase)

Results are interpreted together with the history and clinical findings.

## Give the clinical importance of ALT.

**Alanine aminotransferase** (ALT) is largely cytosolic and more liver-specific than aspartate aminotransferase (AST), so it is more specific for **hepatocellular injury** in most settings. Reference range is typically 4 to 36 IU/L; each laboratory sets its own.

| Degree of rise above upper limit of normal | Causes |
|---|---|
| Borderline (less than 2 times) | Non-specific |
| Mild (2 to 5 times) | MASLD and MASH (metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease and steatohepatitis), chronic alcohol use, chronic hepatitis B or C, drugs (statins, NSAIDs, antiepileptics), early haemochromatosis, extrahepatic causes (muscle disorders, thyroid disease) |
| Moderate (5 to 15 times) | Acute viral hepatitis, autoimmune hepatitis, drug-induced liver injury |
| Severe (more than 15 times) | Acute hepatitis A, B or E, severe drug-induced injury, toxins (**Amanita phalloides**), ischaemic hepatitis |
| Massive (more than 10,000 IU/L) | Paracetamol toxicity, profound ischaemic injury; rarely herpes simplex hepatitis |

- **ALT-predominant pattern**: viral hepatitis, steatohepatitis, acute Budd-Chiari syndrome [hepatic vein thrombosis], ischaemic hepatitis, autoimmune conditions, haemochromatosis, drugs and toxins, alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency, Wilson disease [hereditary copper accumulation from ATP7B mutation], coeliac disease
- The **pattern and trend** matter more than a single value
- Mild elevations are often transient: up to 30% of asymptomatic elevations resolve within 3 weeks, so repeat testing is advised before an extensive work-up
- The size of the rise does not reliably track histological severity or prognosis; in acute liver failure the aminotransferases can improve while synthetic function worsens
- A **normal ALT does not exclude** MASLD or fibrosis

## Give the clinical importance of AST.

**Aspartate aminotransferase** (AST) is found in liver, skeletal muscle, myocardium, kidney, brain, leucocytes and erythrocytes. It exists in cytosolic and mitochondrial forms, which explains its **extrahepatic elevations**. Reference range is typically 5 to 30 IU/L; each laboratory sets its own.

- **AST-predominant pattern**: alcohol-related liver disease, steatohepatitis, cirrhosis
- **Non-hepatic causes of a raised AST**: haemolysis, myopathy, thyroid disease, exercise
- An **AST to ALT ratio of 2 to 1 or greater** suggests chronic alcohol use
- Degree of rise above the upper limit of normal: borderline, less than 2 times; mild, 2 to 5 times; moderate, 5 to 15 times; severe, more than 15 times; massive, more than 10,000 IU/L (paracetamol toxicity, profound ischaemic injury)
- Alcoholic hepatitis usually causes a mild to moderate rise, rarely above 300 to 500 IU/L
- The **pattern and trend** matter more than a single value; mild elevations are often transient
- **Haemolysis falsely raises** AST; a persistent isolated rise may be **macro-AST**, which is benign

## Give the clinical importance of LDH.

**Lactate dehydrogenase** (LDH) is an enzyme present in almost all body tissues. Its five isozymes, LDH-1 to LDH-5, are expressed differently in different tissues, which is the basis of its use as a diagnostic marker.

- **Myocardial infarction**: LDH-1 exceeds LDH-2 (a flipped ratio above 1); LDH-1 stays raised from the second to the fourth day. It has been replaced by troponin as the marker of myocardial infarction
- **Liver injury**: LDH-5 is raised
- Raised in tissue destruction because of cell death and loss of cytoplasm; the level can remain raised for up to 7 days
- Common causes of a raised level: cardiorespiratory disease, malignancy, fracture or trauma, infection, inflammation and hepatitis
- **Limitations**: it is non-specific, so alternative assays are preferred (creatine kinase for muscle, ALT for liver, troponin for heart); haemolysis falsely raises the result because red cells contain LDH

## Give the clinical importance of PSA.

**Prostate-specific antigen** (PSA) is an enzyme made by prostatic epithelial cells. It is released as an inactive precursor, becomes active in the prostatic ducts and then enters the blood. In semen it breaks down the proteins that keep semen gel-like.

- **Normal**: a single upper limit of 4 ng/mL is commonly used; **age-specific limits** are 2.5 ng/mL (40 to 49 years), 3.5 ng/mL (50 to 59), 4.5 ng/mL (60 to 69) and 6.5 ng/mL (70 to 79)
- A value of **4.0 ng/mL or more** is the consensus threshold for further evaluation
- **Causes of a raised PSA**: prostate cancer, benign prostatic hyperplasia (up to 86% of cases), prostatitis, infection, inflammation, trauma, digital rectal examination, urological instrumentation, recent ejaculation, constipation
- **Lowered by**: 5-alpha reductase inhibitors (by about 50% over 6 to 12 months), and possibly statins, NSAIDs, paracetamol and thiazides; obesity lowers PSA by dilution
- **Free PSA**: the free to total ratio falls in malignancy; most useful when total PSA is 4 to 10 ng/mL; free PSA below 10% carries about a 50% cancer risk and above 25% carries below 10% risk
- **PSA density** (total PSA divided by prostate volume): 0.15 or more is suspicious
- **PSA velocity**: a rise above 0.75 ng/mL per year or 25% per year is suspicious; shorter doubling time is linked to higher mortality
- **Screening**: by shared decision after discussing risks and benefits, with a digital rectal examination for an abnormal result
- **Monitoring**: after radical prostatectomy any detectable PSA suggests remaining prostate tissue, and biochemical recurrence is two consecutive values above 0.2 ng/mL; after radiotherapy, biochemical recurrence is two values more than 2 ng/mL above the nadir
- **Limitations**: highly sensitive but **non-specific**, because benign and malignant conditions both raise it; up to 91% of elevated results are not due to cancer; no value rules cancer in or out; it leads to overdiagnosis, overtreatment and unnecessary biopsies; very poorly differentiated, high-Gleason cancers may produce little or none

## Give the clinical importance of serum amylase.

**Amylase** is made mainly by the pancreas (acinar cells, P-type) and the salivary glands (S-type). Pancreatic amylase forms about 40 to 50% of total serum amylase in healthy adults. No international reference range exists; reported intervals run from about 20 to 300 U/L, and each laboratory sets its own.

**Acute pancreatitis**

- Diagnosis needs at least 2 of 3: abdominal pain, amylase or lipase above **3 times** the upper reference limit, and characteristic imaging
- Amylase above 3 times the upper limit strongly suggests acute pancreatitis; at this cut-off P-type amylase is over 90% specific
- It often returns to normal within **3 to 5 days**
- The level does **not predict severity** and does not track the course of the disease
- **Lipase is preferred** because it is more specific and stays raised longer
- A raised amylase with normal lipase suggests an extrapancreatic source or macroamylasaemia
- Hypertriglyceridaemia can falsely lower amylase

**Other causes of a raised amylase**

- Pancreatic: chronic pancreatitis, pancreatic trauma
- Salivary: gland trauma, surgery or radiation, alcoholism, vomiting in anorexia and bulimia
- Abdominal: bowel obstruction, perforation, mesenteric infarction, appendicitis, peritonitis, cholecystitis, ruptured ectopic pregnancy
- Reduced clearance: renal insufficiency, **macroamylasaemia**
- Others: liver disease, diabetic ketoacidosis, pneumonia, cardiac surgery, burns, cerebral trauma, phaeochromocytoma, drugs
- Amylase-producing tumours (lung, ovary, multiple myeloma)

**Macroamylasaemia**: amylase binds to immunoglobulins (mainly IgA or IgG) to form large complexes that are poorly filtered by the kidney. An amylase to creatinine clearance ratio below 1% suggests it, and above 5% supports acute pancreatitis. It is usually benign.

**Low amylase**: cystic fibrosis, chronic pancreatitis, diabetes, obesity, metabolic syndrome, chronic tobacco use; falsely low in tubes containing oxalate, citrate or EDTA.

## Give the clinical importance of acid phosphatase.

**Acid phosphatase** is secreted by prostate tissue (prostatic form), expressed by osteoclasts (bone form) and released in seminal fluid. Normal serum level is about 2 ng/mL or less.

- **Prostatic** acid phosphatase is inhibited by tartrate (tartrate-sensitive)
- **Bone** acid phosphatase is **tartrate-resistant** (TRAP), also called purple acid phosphatase

**Raised in**

- **Prostate cancer**: about 95% of patients, especially with bone metastasis, because cancerous prostate tissue overexpresses the enzyme
- **Bone resorption**: osteoporosis; bone metastases from several cancers raise TRAP
- **Hairy cell leukaemia**: the leukaemic cells contain TRAP, which is sensitive and specific for this disease
- **Gaucher disease** [glucocerebrosidase deficiency, a lysosomal storage disease]: TRAP is a marker when chitotriosidase is normal

**Clinical significance**

- It comes from several tissues, so it is **non-specific**; it is more useful for monitoring therapy and prognosis than for diagnosis
- It was once the main serum marker for prostate cancer but has largely been **replaced by PSA**
- It remains useful in **forensic work** to identify seminal fluid
- TRAP is a candidate marker for bone resorption and for monitoring osteoporosis treatment

## Give the clinical importance of CK-MB.

**Creatine kinase** (CK) has three isoenzymes built from M (muscle) and B (brain) subunits: **MM** (skeletal muscle), **MB** (heart) and **BB** (brain). Skeletal muscle is almost entirely MM; cardiac muscle has about 20 to 30% MB. In healthy people serum CK is mostly MM with small amounts of MB.

- **CK-MB is a more specific indicator of myocardial damage than total CK**
- **Raised in**: myocardial infarction, myocarditis, some cardiac procedures
- A CK-MB (CK-2) above about **6% of total CK** is significant in myocardial infarction
- Total CK in myocardial infarction rises within about 12 hours, peaks at 24 to 36 hours and returns to normal after 48 to 72 hours
- The **CK-MB2 to CK-MB1 isoform ratio** helps separate cardiac from non-cardiac sources: above 1.7 is diagnostic and 1.5 or more strongly suggests infarction; sampling is most useful 8 to 36 hours after onset of symptoms
- **Troponin** is increasingly used instead, because CK is not heart-specific

## Give the clinical importance of alpha-fetoprotein.

**Alpha-fetoprotein** (AFP) is a foetal glycoprotein of about 70 kDa made in the yolk sac, foetal liver and gastrointestinal tract; some adult tumours re-express it. Its half-life is about 4 to 5 days. Normal level in men and non-pregnant women is typically 0 to 40 ng/mL.

**Hepatocellular carcinoma**

- Used for **screening** in high-risk groups: chronic hepatitis B or C, cirrhosis
- In cirrhosis, a level above **200 ng/mL** strongly suggests hepatocellular carcinoma
- **AFP-L3%** (cut-off 10%) has a sensitivity of 75 to 96.9% and a specificity of 90 to 92%; L3 is specific to malignancy, whereas L1 is linked to non-cancerous liver inflammation
- A high AFP or AFP-L3% with a raised bilirubin (above 2 mg/dL) is linked to a poorer prognosis

**Germ cell tumours**

- **Yolk sac tumour** raises AFP; choriocarcinoma raises hCG; embryonal carcinoma raises both
- **Seminoma does not** raise AFP; teratoma raises neither
- About 90% of non-seminomatous testicular tumours have one or both markers raised

**Prenatal screening**: maternal serum AFP is part of the triple and quadruple tests.
