- **Raised**: neural tube defects (spina bifida, anencephaly), abdominal wall defects (omphalocele, gastroschisis), sacrococcygeal teratoma, cystic hygroma, renal abnormalities, osteogenesis imperfecta, threatened abortion
- **Low**: Down syndrome [trisomy 21], trisomy 18, foetal demise, hydatidiform mole

**Other causes of a raised AFP in adults**: metastatic liver cancer, cirrhosis, hepatitis, ataxia-telangiectasia.

**Monitoring**

- A persistently raised level after surgery suggests incomplete removal or metastasis
- Falling levels suggest response to treatment; rising levels suggest failure or recurrence
- In germ cell tumours, response is judged from the fall in AFP (half-life about 5 days) and hCG (12 to 20 hours)

## Give the clinical importance of creatine kinase.

**Creatine kinase** (CK, creatine phosphokinase) regenerates ATP in tissues with high energy demand: heart, skeletal muscle and brain. Its isoenzymes are **MM** (skeletal muscle), **MB** (heart) and **BB** (brain). Skeletal muscle is almost entirely MM; cardiac muscle has about 20 to 30% MB. In healthy people serum CK is mostly MM.

**Causes of a raised CK**

- **Muscle injury or breakdown** (rhabdomyolysis) after crush injury, drugs, viral infection or strenuous exercise
- **Chronic muscle disease**: muscular dystrophy (in Duchenne dystrophy [X-linked dystrophin deficiency] the level can rise before symptoms and in many female carriers), myositis, burns
- Prolonged or intense exercise, with greater rises in untrained people
- **Heart disease**: myocardial infarction, myocarditis, some cardiac procedures
- **Hypothyroidism**
- **Statins**, particularly simvastatin, with higher risk when drugs inhibiting CYP3A4 are taken together
- Non-specific rises in kidney disease and arrhythmias

**Myocardial infarction**: total CK rises within about 12 hours, peaks at 24 to 36 hours and returns to normal after 48 to 72 hours. Because CK is not heart-specific, **CK-MB** and, increasingly, **troponin** are used instead.

**Rhabdomyolysis**

- Muscle pain, weakness and dark urine caused by myoglobin
- CK above **1,000 IU/L** indicates rhabdomyolysis; above **5,000 IU/L** indicates severe disease
- **Acute kidney injury** is the most common complication, so prompt intravenous fluids are given

**Low CK**: seen in connective tissue diseases and reduced physical activity, and linked to higher mortality in chronic kidney disease.

CK is a useful but **non-specific** marker; interpretation depends on sex, age, muscle mass, activity and clinical context.

## What is lipid profile? What are its components and normal range?

The **lipid profile** (lipid panel) is a fasting blood test (after a 9 to 12 hour fast) that includes total cholesterol, HDL cholesterol (HDL-C), LDL cholesterol (LDL-C) and triglycerides; non-HDL cholesterol is also reported in a routine panel.

| Component | How obtained | Classification (mg/dL) |
|---|---|---|
| Total cholesterol | Measured directly | Below 200 desirable; 200 to 239 borderline high; 240 and above high |
| LDL cholesterol | Usually calculated | Below 100 optimal; 100 to 129 near optimal or above optimal; 130 to 159 borderline high; 160 to 189 high; 190 and above very high |
| HDL cholesterol | Measured directly | Below 40 low; 60 and above high |
| Triglycerides | Measured directly | Below 150 normal; 150 to 199 borderline high; 200 to 499 high; 500 and above very high |

- **Friedewald equation**: LDL-C is total cholesterol minus HDL-C minus triglycerides divided by 5 (in mg/dL)
- It becomes inaccurate when triglycerides are high; LDL-C is then measured directly (when fasting triglycerides are above about 200 to 400 mg/dL)
- **Total cholesterol to HDL ratio**: 5 to 1 desirable, 3.5 to 1 optimum
- Without fasting, only total cholesterol and HDL-C are usable
- Abnormal results prompt a search for secondary causes: diabetes, hypothyroidism, liver or kidney disease, and drugs

## What are the fractions of lipid?

**Fractions measured in the lipid profile**

1. **Total cholesterol**
2. **HDL cholesterol**
3. **LDL cholesterol** (usually calculated)
4. **Triglycerides**

**Lipoprotein fractions** that carry plasma lipids

1. **Chylomicrons**: made in the intestine; carry dietary triglycerides
2. **VLDL** (very low-density lipoprotein): made in the liver; carries endogenous triglycerides and some cholesterol
3. **IDL** (intermediate-density lipoprotein): remnant of VLDL; carries cholesterol esters and triglycerides
4. **LDL** (low-density lipoprotein): carries cholesterol esters
5. **HDL** (high-density lipoprotein): carries cholesterol esters; moves excess cholesterol from tissues to the liver

## Write a short note on lipoprotein.

**Lipoproteins** carry lipids through the watery blood, which lipids cannot do alone. Their metabolism determines the serum lipid levels.

| Lipoprotein | Origin and main lipid | Apolipoproteins | Role |
|---|---|---|---|
| Chylomicron | Intestine; dietary triglycerides | A-I, A-II, A-IV, B-48, C-I, C-II, C-III, E | Delivers triglycerides to tissues; remnants are cleared by the liver |
| VLDL | Liver; endogenous triglycerides and some cholesterol | B-100, C-I, C-II, C-III, E | Triglycerides are hydrolysed by lipoprotein lipase in muscle and fat |
| IDL | Remnant of VLDL; cholesterol esters and triglycerides | B-100, C-III, E | Further depleted of triglycerides and converted toward LDL |
| LDL | Cholesterol esters | B-100 | Delivers cholesterol to tissues; cleared by the LDL receptor |
| HDL | Cholesterol esters | A-I, A-II, C-I, C-II, C-III, D, E | Reverse cholesterol transport: moves excess cholesterol from tissues to the liver |

**Functions of apolipoproteins**

- **A-I**: main structural protein of HDL; activates LCAT (lecithin-cholesterol acyltransferase)
- **C-II**: activates lipoprotein lipase
- **C-III**: inhibits lipoprotein lipase
- **E**: ligand for the LDL receptor

**Clinical importance**

- Raised LDL cholesterol is strongly associated with **atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease**
- Apo B-100 correlates with LDL particle number, so higher levels raise the risk of atherosclerosis
- **Familial hypercholesterolaemia** is caused mainly by mutations of the LDL receptor gene
- Severe lipoprotein lipase deficiency causes **chylomicronaemia**, with triglycerides above 2000 mg/dL and a risk of pancreatitis

## Why is HDL good cholesterol and LDL harmful (which fraction is better)?

| Feature | HDL | LDL |
|---|---|---|
| Cholesterol transport | From peripheral tissues to the liver (reverse cholesterol transport) | Delivers cholesterol to tissues |
| Effect on the artery wall | Antiatherogenic and anti-inflammatory; removes cholesterol from foam cells in plaques, which can reduce plaque size and inflammation | Oxidised LDL is deposited in the arterial wall and forms foam cells |
| Main apolipoprotein | A-I | B-100 (correlates with LDL particle number) |
| Association with risk | Low HDL is linked to higher cardiovascular risk | Raised LDL cholesterol is strongly associated with atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease |
| Level | Below 40 mg/dL is low; 60 mg/dL and above is high | Below 100 mg/dL is optimal |

- A **high HDL and a low LDL** are the favourable fractions
- In treatment, **lowering LDL cholesterol is paramount**
- Raising HDL has not been shown to help: HDL-raising therapies have given no clinical benefit, and CETP inhibitors raised HDL by up to 100% yet cardiovascular events rose
- Very high HDL due to CETP deficiency, primary familial hyperalphalipoproteinaemia or endothelial lipase deficiency may not be protective

## How will you diagnose a case of dyslipidaemia?

1. **Define the abnormality**: raised LDL-C, triglycerides, non-HDL-C or lipoprotein(a), or low HDL-C; more than one is often present
2. **History**
   - Diet: saturated and trans fats, simple sugars, alcohol
   - Drugs that raise LDL-C, triglycerides or lipoprotein(a), or lower HDL-C
   - Comorbidities: hypothyroidism, nephrotic syndrome, chronic kidney disease, diabetes, obesity, HIV, inflammatory disease, liver disease
   - A new or suddenly worsening abnormality suggests a secondary cause
   - Anabolic steroid use in young, fit males with very low HDL-C
3. **Family history**: premature cardiovascular disease, lipid disorders, use of lipid-lowering drugs; pancreatitis if triglycerides are markedly raised; a marked abnormality or onset at a young age suggests a genetic disorder
4. **Examination**: look for xanthomas, xanthelasma and corneal arcus
5. **Fasting lipid profile** (after 9 to 12 hours): total cholesterol, triglycerides, HDL-C, calculated LDL-C and non-HDL-C
   - Direct LDL-C is measured if fasting triglycerides are high (above about 200 to 400 mg/dL)
   - LDL-C of 130 to 159 mg/dL is borderline high, 160 to 189 high and 190 or more very high; triglycerides of 150 to 499 mg/dL indicate hypertriglyceridaemia and 500 or more very high; HDL-C below 40 mg/dL is low
6. **Exclude secondary causes**: TSH for raised LDL-C; glycaemic control for raised triglycerides with diabetes; also liver and kidney disease and drugs
7. **Further tests**
   - Lipoprotein(a): premature coronary heart disease, strong family history, familial hypercholesterolaemia, statin resistance, aortic stenosis of uncertain cause, unexplained ischaemic stroke
   - Apo B: above 130 mg/dL is a risk enhancer
   - Genetic testing if monogenic hypercholesterolaemia is suspected, with screening of family members
   - Coronary calcium scan when the decision to start a statin is uncertain

## Name the body fluids usually examined in clinical pathology.

1. **Cerebrospinal fluid**: white cell count, protein, glucose, red cells (haemorrhage); for meningitis, tumours and demyelinating diseases
2. **Pleural fluid**: appearance, protein and LDH (transudate or exudate), leucocytes, pH, triglycerides and cholesterol, adenosine deaminase, amylase
3. **Pericardial fluid**: appearance, total white cell count with differential, cytology, bacteriological smears and cultures
4. **Peritoneal (ascitic) fluid**: appearance, serum-ascites albumin gradient, total protein, polymorphonuclear count, glucose, amylase, triglycerides, urea and creatinine, adenosine deaminase
5. **Amniotic fluid**: appearance and bilirubin (an indirect measure of foetal anaemia); amniocentesis assesses chromosomal anomalies, foetal lung maturity and alloimmunisation
6. **Synovial fluid**: volume, viscosity, clarity, colour, Gram stain, cell count and differential, crystals, culture
7. **Saliva**: antibodies (for example to Helicobacter pylori, Lyme disease [tick-borne Borrelia infection], mumps, measles, HIV-1), hormones (cortisol, testosterone), drugs

## Define atrophy.

**Atrophy** is a reduction in the size of an organ or tissue due to a decrease in cell size and number.

## What do you mean by apoptosis?

**Apoptosis** is a type of cell death that is induced by a tightly regulated suicide programme in which cells destined to die activate intrinsic enzymes that degrade cellular DNA and nuclear and cytoplasmic proteins.

## Types / classification of cellular adaptation.

| Type | Change in the cell |
|---|---|
| **Hypertrophy** | Increase in the size and functional activity of cells |
| **Hyperplasia** | Increase in cell number |
| **Atrophy** | Decrease in the size and metabolic activity of cells |
| **Metaplasia** | Change in the phenotype of cells |

## What is atrophy?

**Atrophy** is a reduction in the size of an organ or tissue due to a decrease in cell size and number.

## Causes of atrophy with examples.

**Physiologic:** common during normal development. The notochord and thyroglossal duct undergo atrophy during foetal development.

**Pathologic**

1. **Decreased workload (disuse atrophy):** skeletal muscle atrophy follows when a fractured bone is immobilised in a plaster cast or a patient is restricted to complete bed rest. With more prolonged disuse the fibres decrease in number, due to apoptosis, as well as in size, and increased bone resorption may lead to disuse osteoporosis.
2. **Loss of innervation (denervation atrophy):** damage to the nerves leads to atrophy of the muscle fibres supplied by those nerves.
3. **Diminished blood supply:** a gradual decrease in blood supply (chronic ischaemia) from slowly developing arterial occlusive disease. In late adult life the brain may undergo progressive atrophy, mainly because of reduced blood supply from atherosclerosis. This is called **senile atrophy**.
4. **Inadequate nutrition:** profound protein-calorie malnutrition (marasmus) leads to marked muscle wasting (cachexia). Cachexia is also seen in chronic inflammatory diseases and cancer, where chronic overproduction of TNF is thought to be responsible.
5. **Loss of endocrine stimulation:** loss of oestrogen stimulation after menopause causes atrophy of the endometrium, vaginal epithelium and breast. The prostate atrophies after chemical or surgical castration.
6. **Pressure:** tissue compression for any length of time can cause atrophy. An enlarging benign tumour can cause atrophy in the surrounding uninvolved tissues, probably from ischaemic changes caused by compromise of the blood supply.

## Differences between hypertrophy and hyperplasia.

| Feature | Hypertrophy | Hyperplasia |
|---|---|---|
| **Definition** | Increase in the size of cells, which increases the size of the organ | Increase in the number of cells in an organ or tissue in response to a stimulus |
| **New cells** | None; just larger cells, from synthesis and assembly of additional intracellular structural components | New cells are formed |
| **Cells involved** | Occurs in tissues with limited proliferative capacity; non-dividing cells such as myocardial fibres increase mass by hypertrophy only | Can take place only if the tissue contains cells capable of dividing, or abundant tissue stem cells |
| **Mechanism** | Increased cellular protein production, from growth factors and direct effects of mechanical force | Growth factor-driven proliferation of mature cells and, in some cases, increased output of new cells from tissue stem cells |
| **Physiologic example** | Gravid uterus; skeletal muscle of bodybuilders | Breast epithelium at puberty and pregnancy; liver regeneration; bone marrow |
| **Pathologic example** | Heart in hypertension or valvular disease | Endometrial hyperplasia; benign prostatic hyperplasia; papillomavirus warts |

**The two frequently** occur together and may be triggered by the same stimuli.

## Define metaplasia.

**Metaplasia** is a reversible change in which one differentiated cell type (epithelial or mesenchymal) is replaced by another cell type.

## What do you mean by cellular aging?

**Cellular ageing** is the result of a progressive decline in cellular function and viability caused by genetic abnormalities and the accumulation of cellular and molecular damage due to the effects of exposure to exogenous influences.

## How does cellular aging occur?

Cellular ageing results from a combination of accumulating cellular damage (for example by free radicals), reduced capacity to divide (replicative senescence), reduced ability to repair damaged DNA, and defective protein homeostasis.

1. **Accumulation of DNA damage**

- Exogenous (physical, chemical and biologic) agents and endogenous factors such as ROS threaten the integrity of nuclear and mitochondrial DNA. Most damage is repaired, but some persists and accumulates as cells age.
- The average haematopoietic stem cell suffers 14 new mutations per year.
- Werner syndrome [a premature ageing syndrome] is caused by a defective DNA helicase, which leads to rapid accumulation of chromosomal damage.
- In Bloom syndrome [inherited DNA-helicase defect with sun-sensitive rash and cancer predisposition] and ataxia-telangiectasia the mutated genes encode proteins involved in repairing double-strand breaks in DNA, and patients show some manifestations of ageing at an increased rate.

2. **Replicative senescence:** all normal cells have a limited capacity for replication, and after a fixed number of divisions they become arrested in a terminally nondividing state.

- **Telomere attrition:** when somatic cells replicate, a small section of the telomere is not duplicated, so telomeres become progressively shorter. The chromosome ends are then seen as broken DNA, which signals cell cycle arrest.
- Telomere length is maintained by **telomerase**, which is expressed in germ cells, present at low levels in stem cells and absent in most somatic tissues. In immortalised cancer cells telomerase is usually reactivated.
- **Activation of tumour suppressor genes:** particularly the CDKN2A locus. Expression of its product p16 (INK4a) correlates with chronological age. By controlling G1 to S phase progression it pushes cells along the senescence pathway.

3. **Defective protein homeostasis**

- Normal folding of proteins (mediated by chaperones) and degradation of misfolded, damaged or unneeded proteins by the autophagy-lysosome and ubiquitin-proteasome systems are both impaired with ageing.
- Mutant mice deficient in heat shock protein chaperones age rapidly, and those that overexpress such chaperones are long lived.
- Rapamycin, which inhibits the mTOR pathway, increases the life span of middle-aged mice.
- Misfolded proteins may accumulate and trigger apoptosis.

4. **Dysregulated nutrient sensing:** caloric restriction increases life span in all eukaryotic species in which it has been tested.

- Caloric restriction is proposed to increase longevity by reducing the signalling intensity of the insulin and IGF-1 pathways and by increasing sirtuins.
- **Sirtuins** are NAD-dependent protein deacetylases. They promote the expression of genes that increase longevity: they inhibit metabolic activity, reduce apoptosis, stimulate protein folding and counteract the harmful effects of oxygen free radicals. Sirtuin-6 also promotes genomic integrity by activating DNA repair enzymes.

5. **Persistent inflammation** is also among the best described mechanisms that promote cellular ageing.

## Define cell injury.

**Cell injury** is the sequence of events that follows when the limits of adaptive responses are exceeded, or when cells are exposed to damaging insults, deprived of critical nutrients, or compromised by mutations that affect essential cellular functions. It is reversible up to a point, but if the injurious stimulus is persistent or severe, the cell suffers irreversible injury and ultimately undergoes cell death.

## Classify cell injury.

1. **Reversible cell injury:** functional and structural alterations in early stages or mild forms of injury, which are correctable if the damaging stimulus is removed.
2. **Irreversible cell injury:** persistent or severe injury, in which the cell passes the point of no return and undergoes cell death, either by necrosis or by apoptosis.

## Causes of cell injury.

1. **Oxygen deprivation (hypoxia):** a deficiency of oxygen that reduces aerobic oxidative respiration; causes are reduced blood flow (ischaemia), inadequate oxygenation of the blood from cardiorespiratory failure, and decreased oxygen-carrying capacity, as in anaemia, carbon monoxide poisoning and severe blood loss.
2. **Physical agents:** mechanical trauma, extremes of temperature (burns and deep cold), sudden changes in atmospheric pressure, radiation and electric shock.
3. **Chemical agents and drugs:** hypertonic glucose or salt, high concentrations of oxygen, poisons such as arsenic, cyanide and mercury, pollutants, pesticides, carbon monoxide, asbestos, alcohol, cocaine and therapeutic drugs.
4. **Infectious agents:** viruses, rickettsiae, bacteria, fungi, parasites and tapeworms.
5. **Immunologic reactions:** reactions to endogenous self-antigens cause autoimmune diseases; reactions to external agents such as viruses and environmental substances also cause injury.
6. **Genetic abnormalities:** an extra chromosome, as in Down syndrome [trisomy 21], or a single base pair substitution, as in sickle cell anaemia; they injure cells through deficient protein function, or through accumulation of damaged DNA or misfolded proteins.
7. **Nutritional imbalances:** protein-calorie deficiency, vitamin deficiencies, anorexia nervosa, and nutritional excess such as obesity and diets high in certain lipids.

## Types of reversible cell injury.

1. **Cellular swelling:** the earliest manifestation of almost all forms of cell injury; it results from influx of water.
2. **Fatty change:** occurs in organs actively involved in lipid metabolism, such as the liver; toxic injury disrupts metabolic pathways and leads to rapid accumulation of triglyceride-filled lipid vacuoles.

## Morphological types of cell injury.

**Reversible injury**

- Cellular swelling (hydropic change or vacuolar degeneration).
- Fatty change.

**Irreversible injury (cell death)**

- Necrosis.
- Apoptosis.

## Differences between coagulative and liquefactive necrosis.

| Feature | Coagulative necrosis | Liquefactive necrosis |
|---|---|---|
| **Tissue change** | Architecture of the dead tissue is preserved for at least some days; firm texture | Dead cells are digested and the tissue becomes a viscous liquid |
| **Mechanism** | Injury denatures structural proteins and enzymes, which blocks proteolysis | Microbes attract leucocytes, which liberate enzymes that digest the cells |
| **Appearance** | Intensely eosinophilic cells with indistinct or reddish nuclei; wedge-shaped yellow kidney infarct | Creamy yellow pus; brain infarct shows dissolution of tissue |
| **Setting** | Ischaemia from vessel obstruction in all organs except the brain; localised area is an infarct | Focal bacterial or occasionally fungal infections; hypoxic death of cells in the central nervous system |

## Define apoptosis.

**Apoptosis** is a type of cell death that is induced by a tightly regulated suicide programme in which cells destined to die activate intrinsic enzymes that degrade cellular DNA and nuclear and cytoplasmic proteins.

## Causes of apoptosis.

**Physiologic situations**

- Removal of supernumerary cells (in excess of the required number) during development. Cell death is critical for involution of primordial structures and remodelling of maturing tissues.
- Involution of hormone-dependent tissues on hormone withdrawal, such as endometrial cell breakdown during the menstrual cycle, ovarian follicular atresia in menopause and regression of the lactating breast after weaning.
- Cell turnover in proliferating cell populations, such as epithelial cells in intestinal crypts, to maintain constant cell numbers (homeostasis).
- Elimination of lymphocytes that fail to produce functional antigen receptors, such as immature lymphocytes in the bone marrow and thymus and germinal centre B cells.
- Elimination of potentially harmful self-reactive lymphocytes, to prevent immune reactions against one's own tissues.
- Death of host cells that have served their useful purpose, such as neutrophils in an acute inflammatory response and lymphocytes at the end of an immune response.

In all of these situations, cells undergo apoptosis because they are deprived of necessary survival signals, such as growth factors and interactions with the extracellular matrix, or they receive pro-apoptotic signals from other cells or the surrounding environment.

**Pathologic conditions**

- **DNA damage:** radiation and cytotoxic anticancer drugs can damage DNA, either directly or through production of free radicals.
- **Accumulation of misfolded proteins:** cell death triggered by improperly folded intracellular proteins and the subsequent ER stress response.
- **Infections**, particularly viral infections, because of the virus itself (as in adenovirus and HIV infections) or the host immune response (as in viral hepatitis).
- Killing by **cytotoxic T lymphocytes**, which is responsible for killing tumour cells, cellular rejection of transplants and tissue damage in graft-versus-host disease.
- Pathologic atrophy in parenchymal organs after duct obstruction, such as occurs in the pancreas, parotid gland and kidney.

## Differences between necrosis and apoptosis.

| Feature | Necrosis | Apoptosis |
|---|---|---|
| **Cell size** | Enlarged (swelling) | Reduced (shrinkage) |
| **Nucleus** | Pyknosis, karyorrhexis, karyolysis | Fragmentation into nucleosome-size fragments |
| **Plasma membrane** | Disrupted | Intact; altered structure, especially orientation of lipids |
| **Cellular contents** | Enzymatic digestion; may leak out of the cell | Intact; may be released in apoptotic bodies |
| **Adjacent inflammation** | Frequent | No |
| **Physiologic or pathologic role** | Usually pathologic (culmination of irreversible cell injury) | Often physiologic, a means of eliminating unwanted cells; may be pathologic after some forms of cell injury, especially DNA damage |
| **Regulation** | Historically thought of as accidental cell death | Regulated cell death, mediated by defined molecular pathways |
| **Cytoplasm** | Eosinophilic, glassy and homogeneous; vacuolated and moth-eaten once organelles are digested | Dense and eosinophilic |

## Differences between dystrophic and metastatic calcification.

| Feature | Dystrophic calcification | Metastatic calcification |
|---|---|---|
| **Tissue involved** | Dying tissues | Otherwise normal tissues |
| **Serum calcium** | Normal; no derangement of calcium metabolism | Hypercalcaemia, secondary to some disturbance in calcium metabolism |
| **Cause** | Necrotic cells and cellular debris that are not promptly destroyed and reabsorbed provide a nidus for deposition of calcium salts | Increased parathyroid hormone secretion; resorption of bone tissue; vitamin D-related disorders; renal failure with retention of phosphate |
| **Sites** | Areas of coagulative, caseous or liquefactive necrosis; foci of enzymatic necrosis of fat; atheromas of advanced atherosclerosis; aging or damaged heart valves; tuberculous lymph node | Interstitial tissues of the gastric mucosa, kidneys, lungs, systemic arteries and pulmonary veins, which all excrete acid and have an internal alkaline compartment |
| **Morphology** | Fine white granules or clumps, gritty; basophilic, amorphous and granular on H&E | Resembles dystrophic calcification; noncrystalline amorphous deposits or hydroxyapatite crystals |
| **Clinical effect** | Often a cause of organ dysfunction, as in calcific valvular disease and atherosclerosis | Usually no clinical dysfunction; massive lung involvement causes respiratory compromise and massive kidney deposits (nephrocalcinosis) may cause renal damage |

## What is granulopoiesis?

**Granulopoiesis** is the stepwise development of neutrophils in the bone marrow, in which granulocyte-monocyte progenitor cells pass through successive stages to become mature neutrophils.

## Stages of the granulocytic series.

1. **Myeloblast:** the earliest precursor recognisable by morphology; delicate nuclear chromatin, two to four nucleoli, and fine azurophilic granules in the cytoplasm.
2. **Promyelocyte:** larger cell with more basophilic cytoplasm and coarse **primary (azurophilic) granules**; round or oval nucleus with one or two nucleoli.
3. **Myelocyte:** **specific (secondary) granules** start to form; this is the stage at which the cell becomes a neutrophilic, eosinophilic or basophilic myelocyte.
4. **Metamyelocyte:** specific granules are fully present; the nucleus is indented.
5. **Band form:** the last stage before the mature cell; the nucleus is band shaped.
6. **Segmented neutrophil:** mature cell; the nucleus has three to five lobes.

As the cell matures, the nuclear to cytoplasmic ratio falls, granule production increases and the nucleus becomes more segmented. Eosinophils and basophils pass through the same stages, named eosinophilic or basophilic before the stage, for example eosinophilic metamyelocyte.

## What do you mean by leucocytosis?

**Leucocytosis** refers to an increase in the number of white cells in the blood. It is a common reaction to a variety of inflammatory states.

## Causes of neutrophilia.

**Causes of neutrophilic leucocytosis**

- **Acute bacterial infections**, especially those caused by pyogenic organisms.
- **Sterile inflammation** caused by tissue necrosis, for example myocardial infarction and burns.
- **Chronic infection or inflammation.**
- **Paraneoplastic**, for example Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells].
- **Myeloproliferative neoplasms**, for example chronic myeloid leukaemia.

**Mechanisms**

- Increased release from marrow stores: in acute inflammation, such as infection, there is a rapid increase in the egress of mature granulocytes from the bone marrow pool, mediated through tumour necrosis factor (TNF) and interleukin-1 (IL-1).
- Increased marrow production: in prolonged infection or inflammation, IL-1, TNF and other mediators stimulate macrophages, marrow stromal cells and T cells to produce more haematopoietic growth factors; these increase proliferation and differentiation of committed granulocytic progenitors and sustain neutrophil production over several days. Granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF) induces neutrophilia.
- Chronic infection or inflammation and paraneoplastic causes are growth factor dependent; myeloproliferative neoplasms are growth factor independent.

## Causes of eosinophilia.

- **Allergic disorders**, such as asthma and hay fever.
- **Parasitic infestations.**
- **Drug reactions.**
- **Certain malignancies**, for example Hodgkin lymphoma [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] and some non-Hodgkin lymphomas.
- **Autoimmune disorders**, for example pemphigus and dermatitis herpetiformis, and some vasculitides.
- **Atheroembolic disease** (transient).

## What is Loeffler syndrome?

**Löffler syndrome** (a self-limited eosinophilic pneumonitis) is a form of secondary eosinophilia caused by invasive infection of the alveoli by the larvae of Ascaris, hookworm or Strongyloides helminth species. It presents with dry cough, dyspnoea and fever.

## Define lymphocytosis.

**Lymphocytosis** is an increase in the number of lymphocytes in the blood, one of the five principal types of leucocytosis.

## What are the causes of lymphocytosis?

**Reactive**

- Disorders associated with chronic immunologic stimulation, where it accompanies monocytosis, for example tuberculosis and brucellosis.
- Viral infections, for example hepatitis A, cytomegalovirus and Epstein-Barr virus [herpesvirus that causes infectious mononucleosis].
- Bordetella pertussis infection.
- Acute viral infections, particularly in children, which cause large numbers of activated lymphocytes that resemble neoplastic lymphoid cells.

**Neoplastic**

- Chronic lymphocytic leukaemia, in which the absolute lymphocyte count is more than 5000 per cubic millimetre.
- Monoclonal lymphocytosis of uncertain significance, a precursor lesion with about a 1% risk per year of progressing to chronic lymphocytic leukaemia.
- T-cell and NK-cell large granular lymphocytic leukaemia, which usually presents with mild-to-moderate lymphocytosis and splenomegaly.

## Write a short note on leukopenia.

**Definition:** leucopenia is a decrease in the number of circulating white cells.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- A low white cell count most commonly results from reduced numbers of **neutrophils** (neutropenia, granulocytopenia).
- **Lymphopenia** is less common. Causes are congenital immunodeficiency diseases, advanced HIV infection, therapy with glucocorticoids or cytotoxic drugs, autoimmune disorders, malnutrition and certain acute viral infections.
- In acute viral infections, lymphopenia stems from lymphocyte redistribution rather than a decrease in the number of lymphocytes in the body. Type I interferons activate T lymphocytes and change the surface proteins that regulate T-cell migration, so activated T cells are sequestered in lymph nodes and adhere more to vascular endothelium.
- An enlarged spleen may trap white cells and induce leucopenia.

**Clinical features and complications**

- Granulocytopenia is more common and is often associated with diminished granulocyte function.
- Its consequences are the infections of neutropenia and agranulocytosis.

## Write a short note on neutropenia.

**Definition:** neutropenia is a reduction in the number of neutrophils in the blood. **Agranulocytosis** is a marked reduction in neutrophils, usually less than 100 per microlitre.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

1. **Inadequate or ineffective granulopoiesis**
   - Suppression of haematopoietic stem cells, as in aplastic anaemia and infiltrative marrow disorders (tumours, granulomatous disease); granulocytopenia is then accompanied by anaemia and thrombocytopenia.
   - Suppression of committed granulocytic precursors by certain drugs.
   - Ineffective haematopoiesis, as in megaloblastic anaemia and myelodysplastic neoplasms, in which defective precursors die in the marrow.
   - Rare congenital conditions, such as severe congenital neutropenia (Kostmann syndrome [severe congenital neutropenia]), in which inherited gene defects impair granulocytic differentiation.
2. **Increased destruction or sequestration of neutrophils**
   - Immune mediated injury, which can be idiopathic, associated with a defined immunologic disorder such as systemic lupus erythematosus, or caused by drugs.
   - Splenomegaly, with sequestration and destruction of neutrophils by increased numbers of splenic macrophages.
   - Increased peripheral utilisation, in overwhelming bacterial, fungal or rickettsial infections.

**Morphology**

- Marrow is hypercellular when neutrophils are destroyed in the periphery or granulopoiesis is ineffective, because of a compensatory increase in granulocytic precursors.
- Marrow is hypocellular when agranulocytosis is caused by agents that suppress or destroy granulocyte precursors.
- Infection is a common consequence. Necrotising ulcerative lesions of the gingiva, floor of the mouth, buccal mucosa, pharynx or elsewhere in the oral cavity are characteristic; they are deep, undermined and covered by grey to green-black necrotic membranes from which bacteria or fungi can be isolated.
- Similar ulcers occur less often in the skin, vagina, anus or gastrointestinal tract.
- Life-threatening invasive bacterial or fungal infections may occur in the lungs, urinary tract and kidneys; deep fungal infections by Candida and Aspergillus are a particular risk.
- Sites of infection often show massive growth of organisms with little leucocytic response.

**Clinical features and complications**

- Malaise, chills and fever, often followed by marked weakness and fatigability.
- In agranulocytosis, infections are often overwhelming and may cause death within hours to days.
- Serious infections are most likely when the neutrophil count falls below 500 per cubic millimetre.
- Broad-spectrum antibiotics must be given promptly when signs or symptoms appear; after myelosuppressive chemotherapy, neutropenia is treated with G-CSF.

## What is agranulocytosis?

**Agranulocytosis** is a marked reduction in neutrophils (usually less than 100 per microlitre), which has the serious consequence of making individuals susceptible to bacterial and fungal infections.

## Important causes of leucopenia, neutropenia and agranulocytosis.

**Leucopenia**

- Neutropenia, the most common cause of a low white cell count.
- Lymphopenia: congenital immunodeficiency, advanced HIV infection, glucocorticoid or cytotoxic drug therapy, autoimmune disorders, malnutrition, certain acute viral infections.
- Splenomegaly, which may trap white cells.

**Neutropenia**

- Suppression of haematopoietic stem cells: aplastic anaemia, infiltrative marrow disorders.
- Suppression of committed granulocytic precursors by certain drugs.
- Ineffective haematopoiesis: megaloblastic anaemia, myelodysplastic neoplasms.
- Congenital: severe congenital neutropenia (Kostmann syndrome [severe congenital neutropenia]).
- Immune mediated injury: idiopathic, systemic lupus erythematosus, drugs; acquired idiopathic neutropenia from autoantibodies against neutrophil-specific antigens.
- Splenomegaly.
- Increased peripheral utilisation in overwhelming bacterial, fungal or rickettsial infections.
- Monoclonal proliferations of large granular lymphocytes (LGL leukaemia), probably through suppression of granulocytic progenitors by products of the neoplastic cell.

**Agranulocytosis**

- **Drug toxicity** is the most common cause.
- Alkylating agents and antimetabolites used in cancer treatment cause it in a predictable, dose-related fashion, through generalised suppression of haematopoiesis.
- Idiosyncratic reaction to a large variety of agents, including certain antibiotics, anticonvulsants, anti-inflammatory drugs, antipsychotic drugs and diuretics.
- Antipsychotic agents such as chlorpromazine and related phenothiazines act through a toxic effect on granulocytic precursors in the marrow.
- Sulfonamides and some other drugs probably act through antibody-mediated destruction of neutrophils.

## Define leukaemoid reaction / write a short note (incl. blood picture).

**Definition:** a leukaemoid reaction is a marked increase in white cells (more than 50,000 per microlitre), typically neutrophils, as an acute inflammatory reaction that can mimic leukaemia. In severe infections many immature granulocytes appear in the blood, mimicking myeloid leukaemia.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Severe infection.
- Intoxication.
- Other malignancies.
- Severe haemorrhage.
- Acute haemolysis.
- It often results from accelerated release of cells from the bone marrow. In acute infection, release of mature granulocytes from the marrow is mediated by TNF and IL-1.

**Blood picture**

- Persistent reactive neutrophilic leucocytosis, with a white cell count of more than 50,000 per microlitre.
- A mixture of early mature neutrophil precursors: myelocytes and metamyelocytes, with promyelocytes and rarely myeloblasts; there is no leukaemic hiatus (the gap between blasts and mature cells seen in acute leukaemia).
- Toxic granulation, cytoplasmic vacuolation and Döhle bodies [sky-blue patches of dilated endoplasmic reticulum].
- A high neutrophil alkaline phosphatase score.
- Normal eosinophil and basophil counts; no monocytosis.
- No Philadelphia chromosome [t(9;22) producing the BCR-ABL1 fusion] or BCR::ABL1 translocation.
- Marrow is hypercellular but otherwise normal.

## What is subleukaemic leukaemia?

**Subleukaemic leukaemia** is a leukaemia with abnormal white cells in the peripheral blood in which the total white cell count is normal.

In **aleukaemic leukaemia** blasts are entirely absent from the blood, so a bone marrow examination is essential to exclude acute leukaemia in pancytopenic patients.

## Write the differences between leukaemoid reaction and leukaemia.

| Feature | Leukaemoid reaction | Leukaemia (chronic myeloid leukaemia) |
|---|---|---|
| **Nature** | Reactive, non-malignant leucocytosis caused by an underlying condition | Neoplastic proliferation |
| **Cells in blood** | Mature neutrophils with a mixture of early precursors | Abnormal granulocytes; a large proportion of myelocytes |
| **Eosinophils and basophils** | Normal count and morphology | Often elevated; may show mixed granulation |
| **Neutrophil morphology** | Toxic granulation and Döhle bodies [pale blue cytoplasmic inclusions in neutrophils] often present | Pseudo-Pelger-Huët forms may be seen |
| **Platelets** | Normal count and morphology | Elevated early and decreased late; giant, hypogranular or bizarre forms |
| **Haemoglobin** | Normal | Often anaemic |
| **Leucocyte alkaline phosphatase score** | Elevated | Decreased |
| **Genetics** | Normal; no BCR::ABL1 | t(9;22) with BCR::ABL fusion gene |
| **Bone marrow** | Hypercellular but otherwise normal | Markedly hypercellular with increased eosinophils and basophils, small dysplastic megakaryocytes and sea-blue histiocytes |
| **Leukaemic hiatus (acute leukaemia)** | Absent | Present in acute leukaemia |

## Differences between leukaemoid reaction and subleukaemic acute leukaemia.

| Feature | Leukaemoid reaction | Subleukaemic acute leukaemia |
|---|---|---|
| **Nature** | Reactive, non-malignant; caused by infection, intoxication, other malignancy, haemorrhage or haemolysis | Neoplastic accumulation of immature blasts |
| **Total white cell count** | Markedly raised, more than 50,000 per microlitre | Normal |
| **Cells in blood** | Myelocytes, metamyelocytes and promyelocytes, rarely myeloblasts, in a mixture with mature neutrophils | Abnormal white cells (blasts) |
| **Leukaemic hiatus** | Absent | Characteristic of acute leukaemia |
| **Neutrophil changes** | Toxic granulation, vacuolation and Döhle bodies [pale blue cytoplasmic inclusions in neutrophils] | Not a feature |
| **Bone marrow** | Hypercellular but otherwise normal | Blasts of 20% or more of marrow cells; the WHO allows fewer with defining genetic aberrations |
| **Genetics** | No BCR::ABL1 | Specific genetic aberrations, such as t(8;21), inv(16) and t(15;17) in acute myeloid leukaemia |
| **Other** | Resolves on treatment of the underlying cause | Needs a bone marrow examination to exclude acute leukaemia |

## Write a short note on leukoerythroblastic blood picture.

**Definition:** leukoerythroblastosis is the premature release into the blood of nucleated erythroid and early granulocyte progenitors (immature precursors).

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

- Processes that distort the marrow architecture, such as deposits of metastatic cancer or granulomatous disorders.
- Fibrosis of the marrow, as in primary myelofibrosis.
- Immature cells also enter the circulation from sites of extramedullary haematopoiesis.

**Blood picture**

- Nucleated red cells and immature granulocytes in the blood.
- Teardrop-shaped red cells (dacryocytes), which are probably damaged during the birthing process in the fibrotic marrow.
- In myelofibrosis, moderate to severe normochromic normocytic anaemia; large platelets and basophilia are common but nonspecific.

**Clinical significance**

- It is characteristic of primary myelofibrosis but is seen in other infiltrative disorders of the marrow, including granulomatous diseases and metastatic tumours.
- The blood findings are not specific; bone marrow biopsy is essential for diagnosis.

## Causes of leukoerythroblastic blood picture.

- **Primary myelofibrosis**, and the spent phase of other myeloproliferative neoplasms.
- **Metastatic tumours** in the marrow.
- **Granulomatous diseases** of the marrow.
- Other **infiltrative disorders** of the marrow.
- Marrow infiltration by lymphoid tumour, for example chronic lymphocytic leukaemia, together with premature release of progenitors in the face of severe anaemia.

## Define leukaemia.

**Leukaemias** are neoplasms that present with widespread involvement of the bone marrow and (usually, but not always) the peripheral blood.

## Classify (acute) leukaemia.

| Type | Cell of origin | Subtypes |
|---|---|---|
| **Acute lymphoblastic leukaemia/lymphoma (ALL)** | Immature B (pre-B) or T (pre-T) cells, called lymphoblasts | B-cell ALL (about 85%); T-cell ALL |
| **Acute myeloid leukaemia (AML)** | Haematopoietic progenitors; immature myeloid blasts | WHO class I: AML with specific genetic aberrations. WHO class II: AML defined by differentiation |

## FAB classification of AML.

| Type | Name |
|---|---|
| **M0** | Acute myeloblastic leukaemia with minimal differentiation |
| **M1** | Myeloblastic leukaemia without maturation |
| **M2** | Myeloblastic leukaemia with maturation |
| **M3** | Acute promyelocytic leukaemia |
| **M4** | Acute myelomonocytic leukaemia; the myeloblastic and monoblastic components each make up more than 20% of the blasts in the marrow |
| **M5** | Acute monoblastic leukaemia: M5a without differentiation, M5b with differentiation |
| **M6** | Erythroleukaemia |
| **M7** | Acute megakaryoblastic leukaemia |

## WHO classification of AML.

| Subtype | Prognosis | Morphology or comment |
|---|---|---|
| **I. AML with specific genetic aberrations** | | |
| t(8;21)(q22;q22); RUNX1::RUNX1T1 | Favourable | Full range of myelocytic maturation; Auer rods [needle-shaped azurophilic inclusions in myeloblasts] easily found; abnormal cytoplasmic granules |
| inv(16)(p13;q22); CBFB::MYH11 | Favourable | Myelocytic and monocytic differentiation; abnormal eosinophilic precursors with abnormal basophilic granules |
| t(15;17)(q22;q11-12); PML::RARA (acute promyelocytic leukaemia) | Very favourable | Numerous Auer rods, often in bundles; prominent primary granules, inconspicuous in the microgranular variant; high incidence of disseminated intravascular coagulation |
| t(11q23;v); KMT2A rearrangement | Poor | Usually some monocytic differentiation |
| Mutated NPM1 | Favourable | Detected by DNA sequencing |
| Myelodysplasia-related genetic abnormalities | Poor | Deletion of chromosome 5q or 7q, or mutations in SRSF2, SF3B1, U2AF1, ZRSR2, ASXL1, EZH2, BCOR or STAG2 |
| **II. AML defined by differentiation** | | |
| Minimally differentiated | Intermediate | Negative for myeloperoxidase; myeloid antigens detected on blasts by flow cytometry |
| Without maturation | Intermediate | More than 3% of blasts positive for myeloperoxidase |
| With maturation | Intermediate | Full range of myelocytic maturation |
| With myelomonocytic maturation | Intermediate | Myelocytic and monocytic differentiation |
| With monocytic maturation | Intermediate | Nonspecific esterase positive monoblasts and promonocytes predominate in the marrow |
| With erythroid maturation | Intermediate | Erythroid/myeloid subtype: more than 50% dysplastic maturing erythroid precursors and more than 20% myeloblasts; pure erythroid subtype: more than 80% erythroid precursors without myeloblasts |
| With megakaryocytic maturation | Intermediate | Megakaryocytic blasts predominate, detected with antibodies such as GPIIb/IIIa; often marrow fibrosis; most common AML in Down syndrome [trisomy 21] |

## Clinical presentation of acute leukaemia.

- **Abrupt, stormy onset** within days to a few weeks of the first symptoms.
- **Marrow failure**: fatigue from anaemia; fever from infections secondary to neutropenia; bleeding from thrombocytopenia.
- **Bleeding**: cutaneous petechiae and ecchymoses, serosal haemorrhages, and mucosal haemorrhages into the gingivae and urinary tract. Procoagulants and fibrinolytic factors released by leukaemic cells, especially in AML with t(15;17), make bleeding worse.
- **Infections**, frequent in the oral cavity, skin, lungs, kidneys, urinary bladder and colon, often caused by opportunists such as fungi, Pseudomonas and commensal organisms.
- **Mass effects from neoplastic infiltration**, more common in ALL: bone pain, generalised lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, hepatomegaly, testicular enlargement, and in T-ALL compression of large vessels and airways in the mediastinum.
- **Central nervous system** manifestations, more common in ALL: headache, vomiting and nerve palsies from meningeal spread.
- In AML, tumours with monocytic differentiation often infiltrate the skin (leukaemia cutis) and gingiva; AML occasionally presents as a localised soft tissue mass, a myeloid sarcoma.

## How will you diagnose a case of acute leukaemia in the laboratory?

1. **Peripheral blood:** blast count is highly variable; in AML blasts may be more than 100,000 per microlitre but are under 10,000 in about 50% of patients, and occasionally blasts are absent from the blood (aleukaemic leukaemia).
2. **Bone marrow examination** is essential. The marrow is hypercellular and packed with blasts, generally 20% or more of marrow cells.
3. **Morphology of blasts:** lymphoblasts have condensed chromatin, small nucleoli and scant agranular cytoplasm; myeloblasts have delicate chromatin, two to four nucleoli and more cytoplasm with fine azurophilic granules and often Auer rods [needle-shaped azurophilic inclusions in myeloblasts].
4. **Cytochemistry:** myeloperoxidase positive in myeloblasts; lymphoblasts are myeloperoxidase negative and often contain periodic acid–Schiff positive cytoplasmic material; monoblasts are nonspecific esterase positive.
5. **Immunophenotyping by flow cytometry**, which confirms the lineage. Terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase (TdT) is positive in more than 95% of ALL. B-ALL: CD19, CD10, PAX5; T-ALL: CD1, CD2, CD5, CD7. Myeloid blasts: CD34, CD33, CD15 and CD64.
6. **Cytogenetics and molecular tests:** chromosomal aberrations in 50% to 70% of AML by standard techniques; approximately 90% of ALL have numerical or structural chromosomal changes.

## Differences between AML and ALL (including by PBF).

| Feature | AML | ALL |
|---|---|---|
| **Cell of origin** | Immature myeloid cells (myeloblasts, monoblasts) | Immature B (pre-B) or T (pre-T) lymphoblasts |
| **Blast chromatin** | Delicate | More condensed |
| **Nucleoli** | Two to four; prominent | Small; less conspicuous |
| **Cytoplasm** | More voluminous; fine azurophilic granules | Scant; usually no granules |
| **Auer rods** [needle-shaped azurophilic inclusions in myeloblasts] | Present in many cases; numerous in t(15;17) | Not a feature |
| **Myeloperoxidase** | Positive | Negative |
| **Periodic acid–Schiff** | Not described | Often positive cytoplasmic material |
| **TdT** | Not expressed | Positive in more than 95% |
| **Blood blasts** | More than 100,000 per microlitre to absent; under 10,000 in about 50% | High counts, with more than 100,000 a poor prognostic sign |
| **Age** | Incidence rises through life, peaking after 60 years | Most common cancer of children; B-ALL peaks at about 3 years |
| Mass effects and central nervous system spread | Less striking and less common | More common: lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, hepatomegaly, testicular enlargement, mediastinal mass in T-ALL, meningeal spread |
| **Bleeding** | Often prominent; worsened in t(15;17) | Thrombocytopenic bleeding |
| **Outcome** | About 60% remission; 15% to 30% disease free at 5 years | About 95% of children remission; 75% to 85% cured |

## Laboratory diagnosis of AML.

1. **Peripheral blood:** blast count highly variable; may be more than 100,000 per microlitre but under 10,000 in about 50%; occasionally absent (aleukaemic leukaemia). Anaemia, neutropenia and thrombocytopenia are present.
2. **Bone marrow examination** is essential to exclude acute leukaemia in pancytopenic patients. Generally 20% or more myeloid blasts; the WHO permits the diagnosis with fewer than 20% if a defining genetic aberration is present.
3. **Blast morphology:** myeloblasts have delicate nuclear chromatin, two to four nucleoli and more cytoplasm than lymphoblasts, with fine peroxidase positive azurophilic granules. **Auer rods** [needle-shaped azurophilic inclusions in myeloblasts] (needle-like azurophilic granules) are present in many cases, particularly numerous in t(15;17). Monoblasts have folded or lobulated nuclei, lack Auer rods and are nonspecific esterase positive.
4. **Immunophenotyping:** stains for myeloid-specific antigens confirm the diagnosis, since myeloblasts and lymphoblasts are hard to tell apart by morphology; blasts express CD34, CD33 and in part CD15, but not CD64. Megakaryocytic blasts are detected with antibodies such as GPIIb/IIIa.
5. **Cytogenetics:** abnormalities are detected in 50% to 70% of cases with standard techniques and about 90% with high-resolution banding. Balanced translocations t(8;21), inv(16) and t(15;17) are typical of de novo AML in younger adults; deletions or monosomies of chromosomes 5 and 7 and p53 mutations follow DNA-damaging agents.
6. **DNA sequencing** detects mutations such as NPM1.
## Why is infection common in acute leukaemia?

- Accumulation of neoplastic blasts in the bone marrow suppresses normal haematopoiesis by physical crowding, competition for growth factors and other poorly understood mechanisms.
- This depresses marrow function, so **neutropenia** develops; fever reflects infections secondary to neutropenia.
- Neutropenia leads to infections, which are often fulminant.
- Infections are frequent in the oral cavity, skin, lungs, kidneys, urinary bladder and colon.
- They are often caused by **opportunists** such as fungi, Pseudomonas and commensal organisms.

## How will you differentiate myeloblast from lymphoblast?

| Feature | Myeloblast | Lymphoblast |
|---|---|---|
| **Nuclear chromatin** | Delicate | More condensed |
| **Nucleoli** | Two to four; prominent | Less conspicuous; small and sharply demarcated by a rim of condensed chromatin |
| **Cytoplasm** | More voluminous; often fine peroxidase positive azurophilic granules | Smaller amount; scant basophilic; usually lacks granules |
| **Auer rods** [needle-shaped azurophilic inclusions in myeloblasts] | Present in many cases | Not described |
| **Myeloperoxidase** | Positive | Negative |
| **Periodic acid–Schiff** | Not described | Often positive cytoplasmic material |
| **Terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase (TdT)** | Not described | Positive in more than 95% of cases; expressed only in pre-B and pre-T lymphoblasts |
| **Surface markers** | CD34, CD33, CD15 | B lineage: CD19, CD10, PAX5. T lineage: CD1, CD2, CD5, CD7 |

**These morphological distinctions** are not absolute. The definitive diagnosis relies on stains with antibodies specific for B-cell, T-cell and myeloid antigens.

## What is Philadelphia chromosome?

The **Philadelphia chromosome** [t(9;22) producing the BCR-ABL1 fusion] (Ph) is the reciprocal (9;22)(q34;q11) translocation that creates the chimeric BCR::ABL gene, from portions of the BCR gene on chromosome 22 and the ABL gene on chromosome 9. It is present in more than 90% of cases of chronic myeloid leukaemia.

## Importance of the Philadelphia chromosome.

- It is the hallmark of **chronic myeloid leukaemia (CML)**, which is distinguished from other myeloproliferative neoplasms by the chimeric BCR::ABL gene.
- BCR::ABL directs the synthesis of a **constitutively active BCR-ABL tyrosine kinase**, usually 210 kDa in size. The BCR part has a dimerisation domain that self-associates and activates the ABL kinase.
- The kinase drives the RAS and JAK/STAT pathways, the same progrowth and prosurvival pathways turned on by haematopoietic growth factors. It preferentially drives proliferation of granulocytic and megakaryocytic progenitors and causes abnormal release of immature granulocytes from the marrow.
- It shows that the **cell of origin is a pluripotent haematopoietic stem cell**.
- Diagnosis: BCR::ABL detected by chromosomal analysis or PCR-based tests. In the remaining cases, where the fusion gene is formed by complex or cryptic rearrangements, it is detected by fluorescence in situ hybridisation (FISH) or PCR.
- Treatment: BCR-ABL inhibitors give sustained haematological remissions in more than 90% of patients. Sensitive molecular tests monitor for recurrence.
- BCR::ABL1 is also found in a subset of B-cell acute lymphoblastic leukaemia.

## What do you mean by blast crisis / blastic transformation?

- Blast crisis is the terminal event in CML, a picture resembling **acute leukaemia**.
- About 50% of patients first enter an **accelerated phase** after a variable period averaging 3 years, with increasing anaemia and thrombocytopenia, sometimes a rise in basophils, and new cytogenetic abnormalities such as trisomy 8, isochromosome 17q or duplication of the Ph chromosome. Within 6 to 12 months this ends in blast crisis.
- In the other 50%, blast crisis occurs abruptly without an accelerated phase.
- In 70% of crises the blasts are myeloid (**myeloid blast crisis**); in most of the remainder they are of pre-B cell origin (**lymphoid blast crisis**). This is evidence that CML originates from a pluripotent stem cell with both myeloid and lymphoid potential.
- In lymphoid blast crisis, 85% of cases have mutations that interfere with Ikaros, a transcription factor encoded by IKZF1.

## Laboratory diagnosis of CGL/CML.

1. **Peripheral blood**
   - Leucocytosis, often exceeding 100,000 per microlitre, of neutrophils, band forms, metamyelocytes, myelocytes, eosinophils and basophils.
   - Blasts usually less than 10% of circulating cells.
   - Platelets usually increased, sometimes markedly.
   - Mild to moderate anaemia.
2. **Bone marrow**
   - Markedly hypercellular, with massively increased maturing granulocytic precursors, usually with a raised proportion of eosinophils and basophils.
   - Megakaryocytes increased, usually small and dysplastic.
   - Erythroid progenitors normal or mildly decreased.
   - Scattered **sea-blue histiocytes** (macrophages with abundant wrinkled green-blue cytoplasm).
   - Increased reticulin; overt fibrosis is rare.
3. **Molecular and cytogenetic tests:** the BCR::ABL fusion gene, with the Philadelphia chromosome [t(9;22) producing the BCR-ABL1 fusion] t(9;22), detected by chromosomal analysis, FISH or PCR-based tests; this differentiates CML from other myeloproliferative neoplasms.

## Why is the prognosis of blast crisis worse than classical CGL?

- **Untreated classical CML** progresses slowly, with a median survival of about 3 years.
- **Blast crisis is** the greatest threat to the patient.
- **It resembles acute leukaemia**.
- **The accelerated phase** brings additional clonal cytogenetic abnormalities: trisomy 8, isochromosome 17q, duplication of the Ph chromosome.
- **Lymphoid blast crisis** has mutations in Ikaros in 85% of cases.
- **BCR-ABL inhibitors control** blood counts and decrease the risk of transformation to the accelerated phase and blast crisis.

## Laboratory diagnosis of CLL.

1. **Peripheral blood**
   - Absolute lymphocyte count more than 5000 per cubic millimetre (the diagnostic requirement for CLL).
   - Variable numbers of small round lymphocytes with scant cytoplasm and condensed chromatin.
   - **Smudge cells**, tumour cells disrupted in making smears.
   - Leucocyte count highly variable; counts above 200,000 per cubic millimetre are sometimes seen.
   - With autoimmune haemolytic anaemia, spherocytes; with marrow infiltration, nucleated red cells.
2. **Lymph node:** diffuse effacement by small lymphocytes 6 to 12 mm in diameter with round to slightly irregular nuclei, condensed chromatin and scant cytoplasm. **Proliferation centres** of larger activated lymphocytes are pathognomonic. The bone marrow, spleen and liver are also involved.
3. **Immunophenotype:** CD19, CD20, CD23 and CD5; low-level surface immunoglobulin, usually IgM, or IgM and IgD; high-level BCL2.
4. **Cytogenetics:** deletions of 13q14.3, 11q and 17p, and trisomy 12; NOTCH1 mutations in 10% to 18%.
5. **Prognostic markers:** deletions of 11q and 17p (involving TP53), lack of somatic hypermutation, ZAP-70 expression and NOTCH1 mutations indicate a worse outcome.
6. A small monoclonal immunoglobulin spike is present in some patients; hypogammaglobulinaemia is common.

## What do you know about myelodysplastic syndrome?

**Definition:** myelodysplastic neoplasms (MDS, formerly myelodysplastic syndromes) are clonal stem cell disorders characterised by maturation defects that cause ineffective haematopoiesis and carry a high risk of transformation to AML. The marrow is partly or wholly replaced by the clonal progeny of a neoplastic multipotent stem cell that differentiates in an ineffective and disordered fashion, so patients have peripheral blood cytopenias. MDS may be primary (idiopathic) or secondary to previous genotoxic drug or radiation therapy (t-MDS), which usually appears 2 to 8 years after exposure.

**Pathogenesis**

- Driver mutations overlap partly with those of AML: **epigenetic factors** (DNA methylation, histone modifications, chromatin looping), **RNA splicing factors**, and **transcription factors** such as RUNX1 (mostly loss of function).
- About 10% have loss-of-function TP53 mutations, with a complex karyotype and poor outcome.
- Recurrent chromosomal abnormalities: monosomies 5 and 7, deletions of 5q, 7q and 20q, and trisomy 8. The region lost on 5q contains RPS14; loss of one copy produces ineffective erythropoiesis.

**Morphology**

- Marrow usually hypercellular; dysplastic differentiation of the erythroid, granulocytic, monocytic and megakaryocytic lineages.
- Erythroid: **ring sideroblasts**, megaloblastoid maturation, nuclear budding.
- Neutrophils: decreased secondary granules, toxic granulations, Döhle bodies [pale blue cytoplasmic inclusions in neutrophils], and **pseudo-Pelger-Hüet cells** (neutrophils with only two nuclear lobes).
- Megakaryocytes with single nuclear lobes or multiple separate nuclei (pawn ball megakaryocytes).
- Marrow blasts increased but less than 20%; blood blasts usually less than 10%.
- Blood: pseudo-Pelger-Hüet cells, giant platelets, macrocytes, poikilocytes and relative or absolute monocytosis.

**Clinical features and complications**

- Mainly a disease of older adults; mean age of onset is 70 years; up to half are found incidentally on routine blood tests.
- Symptoms are weakness, infections and haemorrhages from pancytopenia.
- All forms can transform to AML, most frequently and rapidly in t-MDS.
- Median survival in primary MDS ranges from less than 6 months to more than 5 years; in t-MDS it is only 4 to 8 months.
- Worse outcomes are predicted by a higher blast count, more severe cytopenias and complex cytogenetics.

## What are myeloproliferative disorders?

**Myeloproliferative neoplasms** are myeloid neoplasms in which there is usually increased production of one or more types of blood cells. Their common pathogenic feature is mutated, constitutively activated tyrosine kinases, or other acquired aberrations in signalling pathways, that lead to growth factor independence. Differentiation is not impaired, so the usual result is an increase in mature blood elements.

Common features:

- Increased proliferative drive in the bone marrow.
- Variable homing of neoplastic stem cells to secondary haematopoietic organs, producing extramedullary haematopoiesis.
- Variable transformation to a spent phase with marrow fibrosis and peripheral blood cytopenias.
- Variable transformation to acute leukaemia.

## Classify myeloproliferative disorder.

| Disorder | Mutation (frequency) |
|---|---|
| **Chronic myeloid leukaemia** | BCR::ABL fusion gene (100%) |
| **Polycythaemia vera** | JAK2 mutations (more than 95%) |
| **Essential thrombocythaemia** | JAK2 (50% to 60%), CALR (25% to 35%), MPL (5% to 10%) |
| **Primary myelofibrosis** | JAK2 (50% to 60%), CALR (25% to 35%), MPL (5% to 10%) |
| **Systemic mastocytosis** | KIT mutations (more than 90%) |
| **Chronic eosinophilic leukaemia** | FIP1L1::PDGFRA fusion gene (common); PDE4DIP::PDGFRB fusion gene (rare) |
| Myeloid/lymphoid neoplasms with eosinophilia and tyrosine kinase gene mutations | Fusion genes involving FGFR1, PDGFRA, PDGFRB or JAK2 (100%) |

## What do you mean by polycythaemia?

**Polycythaemia** denotes an abnormally high number of circulating red cells, usually with a corresponding increase in the haemoglobin level. It may be relative (when there is haemoconcentration due to decreased plasma volume) or absolute (when there is an increase in the total red cell mass).

## Clinical features of polycythaemia.

Clinical features of polycythaemia vera:

- Insidious onset, usually in adults of late middle age.
- Increased red cell mass, haematocrit and total blood volume cause abnormal blood flow, with distension of the low-pressure venous side.
- Patients are **plethoric and cyanotic**, from stagnation and deoxygenation of blood in peripheral vessels.
- Headache, dizziness, hypertension and gastrointestinal symptoms.
- **Intense pruritus** and **peptic ulceration**, both possibly from histamine release by basophils.
- **Hyperuricaemia** from high cell turnover; symptomatic secondary gout in 5% to 10%.
- **Thrombosis:** about 25% first come to attention with deep venous thrombosis, myocardial infarction or stroke; hepatic vein thrombosis (Budd-Chiari syndrome [hepatic vein thrombosis], thrombosis of the hepatic veins) and portal or mesenteric vein thrombosis with bowel infarction.
- **Bleeding:** minor haemorrhages (epistaxis, bleeding gums) are common; life-threatening haemorrhages occur in 5% to 10%.
- Mild organomegaly, caused early in the course largely by congestion.

## Differences between polycythaemia vera and secondary polycythaemia.

| Feature | Polycythaemia vera | Secondary polycythaemia |
|---|---|---|
| **Nature** | Primary; myeloproliferative neoplasm with an intrinsic abnormality of haematopoietic precursors | Absolute polycythaemia from a response of red cell progenitors to raised erythropoietin |
| **Cause** | Activating mutations in the tyrosine kinase JAK2 (more than 97% with a valine-to-phenylalanine change at residue 617) | Compensatory or pathologic increase in erythropoietin secretion: erythropoietin-secreting tumours (renal cell carcinoma, hepatocellular carcinoma, cerebellar haemangioblastoma), high oxygen affinity haemoglobin mutants, inherited defects that stabilise HIF-1α |
| **Serum erythropoietin** | Low | High |
| **Erythropoietin dependence** | Progenitors have markedly decreased requirements for erythropoietin and other growth factors | Red cell progenitors respond to erythropoietin |
| **Cell lines** | Increased red cells, granulocytes and platelets (panmyelosis) | Red cells |

## Laboratory diagnosis of polycythaemia.

1. **Red cell parameters:** haemoglobin typically more than 16 g/dL; haematocrit often 55% or more. Chronic bleeding may cause iron deficiency, which can lower the haematocrit into the normal range.
2. **White cell count** 12,000 to 50,000 per cubic millimetre; **platelet count** often more than 500,000 per cubic millimetre, with giant forms and defective aggregation on functional tests.
3. **Peripheral blood:** increased basophils and abnormally large platelets.
4. **Serum erythropoietin:** low in polycythaemia vera, high in secondary forms.
5. **JAK2 mutation:** more than 97% have the valine-to-phenylalanine substitution at residue 617; other JAK2 mutations are found in most of the rest.
6. **Bone marrow:** hypercellular with some residual fat; a subtle increase in red cell progenitors, usually with increased granulocytic precursors and megakaryocytes; a moderate to marked increase in reticulin fibres in about 10%.

## Non-CML myeloproliferative disorders.

| Disorder | Mutations | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Polycythaemia vera** | JAK2 in more than 95% (more than 97% at residue 617) | Increased red cells, granulocytes and platelets; plethora, pruritus, thrombosis and bleeding; low serum erythropoietin; spent phase in 15% to 20% and transformation to AML in about 1% to 2% |
| **Essential thrombocythaemia** | JAK2 (50% to 60%), CALR (about 30%), MPL (5% to 10%) | Raised platelet count with thrombosis or bleeding; no polycythaemia and no marrow fibrosis; erythromelalgia |
| **Primary myelofibrosis** | JAK2, CALR or MPL in about 90% | Obliterative marrow fibrosis, leukoerythroblastosis, teardrop red cells, splenomegaly from extramedullary haematopoiesis |

## WHO diagnostic criteria for polycythaemia vera.

The WHO 2016 criteria. The diagnosis requires either all three major criteria, or the first two major criteria and the minor criterion.

**Major criteria**

1. **Haemoglobin** more than 16.5 g/dL in men or more than 16.0 g/dL in women, **or haematocrit** more than 49% in men or more than 48% in women, or red cell mass more than 25% above the mean normal predicted value.
2. **Bone marrow biopsy** showing hypercellularity for age with trilineage growth (panmyelosis), including prominent erythroid, granulocytic and megakaryocytic proliferation with pleomorphic mature megakaryocytes.
3. Presence of a **JAK2 V617F** or **JAK2 exon 12** mutation.

**Minor criterion**

- **Subnormal serum erythropoietin** level.

Bone marrow biopsy may not be required in cases with sustained absolute erythrocytosis, with haemoglobin more than 18.5 g/dL in men (haematocrit more than 55.5%) or more than 16.5 g/dL in women (haematocrit more than 49.5%), if the third major criterion and the minor criterion are present.

## Define myelofibrosis.

The hallmark of **primary myelofibrosis** is the development of obliterative marrow fibrosis. The replacement of the marrow by fibrous tissue reduces bone marrow haematopoiesis, leading to cytopenias and extensive extramedullary haematopoiesis.

## Laboratory diagnosis of myelofibrosis.

1. **Peripheral blood**
   - Moderate to severe **normochromic normocytic anaemia**.
   - **Leukoerythroblastosis**: premature release of nucleated erythroid and early granulocyte progenitors.
   - **Teardrop-shaped red cells** (dacryocytes).
   - White cell count usually normal or mildly reduced, but may be markedly elevated (80,000 to 100,000 per microlitre) early in the course.
   - Platelet count usually normal or elevated at diagnosis; thrombocytopenia may supervene as the disease progresses.
   - Abnormally large platelets and basophilia, which are nonspecific.
2. **Bone marrow biopsy** is essential for diagnosis, because the blood findings are not specific.
   - Early: hypercellular, with increases in maturing cells of all lineages; large megakaryocytes with bulbous, hyperchromatic or cloud-like nuclei, abnormally clustered; minimal fibrosis.
   - Later: hypocellular and diffusely fibrotic; clusters of atypical megakaryocytes; haematopoietic elements within dilated sinusoids; very late, conversion of the marrow space to bone (osteosclerosis).
3. **Mutations:** about 90% have activating mutations of JAK2, CALR or MPL.

## Types of myelofibrosis.

1. **Primary myelofibrosis:** a myeloproliferative neoplasm whose hallmark is obliterative marrow fibrosis, with activating mutations of JAK2, CALR or MPL in about 90%.
2. **Secondary myelofibrosis (spent phase):** the marrow fibrosis that develops late in other myeloproliferative neoplasms, such as polycythaemia vera and essential thrombocythaemia. The histological appearance is identical to primary myelofibrosis.

## Conditions associated with secondary myelofibrosis.

- **Polycythaemia vera:** the disease passes into a spent phase of obliterative marrow fibrosis with extensive extramedullary haematopoiesis, mainly in the spleen, in about 15% to 20% of patients after an average period of 10 years. Patients with two mutated copies of JAK2 progress at a greater rate.
- **Essential thrombocythaemia:** a spent phase of marrow fibrosis supervenes uncommonly.
- Other myeloproliferative neoplasms: variable transformation to a spent phase with marrow fibrosis and peripheral blood cytopenias; in chronic myeloid leukaemia overt marrow fibrosis is rare.

## Essential thrombocythaemia (in short).

**Definition:** a myeloproliferative neoplasm with an elevated platelet count, separated from polycythaemia vera and primary myelofibrosis by the absence of polycythaemia and marrow fibrosis respectively.

**Pathogenesis**

- Over 90% have activating mutations in **JAK2** (50% to 60%), **MPL** (5% to 10%) or **CALR** (about 30%).
- Constitutive JAK2 or MPL signalling makes the progenitors thrombopoietin independent and leads to hyperproliferation.
- Mutated CALR is secreted and binds and activates the thrombopoietin receptor, an oncogenic autocrine feedback loop.
- In cases without a mutation, causes of reactive thrombocytosis, such as inflammatory disorders and iron deficiency, must be excluded.

**Morphology**

- Marrow cellularity usually only mildly increased; megakaryocytes markedly increased, including abnormally large forms; delicate reticulin fibrils, without overt fibrosis.
- Blood: abnormally large platelets, often with mild leucocytosis.
- Mild organomegaly in about 50% from extramedullary haematopoiesis.

**Clinical features and complications**

- Incidence 1 to 3 per 100,000 per year; usually past the age of 60 years.
- **Thrombosis and haemorrhage** from platelet dysfunction: deep venous thrombosis, portal and hepatic vein thrombosis, myocardial infarction and abnormal bleeding.
- Up to one-third have acquired von Willebrand disease [inherited bleeding disorder from deficient or defective von Willebrand factor].
- **Erythromelalgia**, a throbbing and burning of hands and feet from occlusion of small arterioles by platelet aggregates.
- Indolent; median survival 12 to 15 years; uncommonly a spent phase of marrow fibrosis or transformation to AML.

## Causes of hypersplenism.

**Hypersplenism** is a syndrome of splenomegaly with anaemia, leucopenia and/or thrombocytopenia. The probable cause of the cytopenias is increased sequestration of formed elements and the consequent enhanced phagocytosis by splenic macrophages.

Causes are the disorders associated with splenomegaly:

- **Congestive splenomegaly** from portal or splenic vein hypertension: cirrhosis (the main cause of massive congestive splenomegaly), the pipestem hepatic fibrosis of schistosomiasis, portal vein thrombosis, splenic vein thrombosis from tumours of the stomach or pancreas, and right-sided heart failure. In congestive splenomegaly, slowing of blood flow from the cords to the sinusoids prolongs exposure of blood cells to macrophages, resulting in excessive destruction.
- **Infections:** blood-borne infections, particularly infective endocarditis, and infectious mononucleosis.
- **Haematological:** non-Hodgkin [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] lymphomas, lymphocytic leukaemias, multiple myeloma, myeloproliferative neoplasms and haemolytic anaemias.
- **Infiltrative disorders:** sarcoidosis, amyloidosis, glycogen storage and lipid storage diseases, and metastases.

## Causes of splenomegaly.

1. **Infections:** nonspecific splenitis of any blood-borne infection, particularly infective endocarditis; infectious mononucleosis; malaria; typhoid fever.
2. **Haematological disorders:** non-Hodgkin [lymphoma defined by Reed-Sternberg cells] lymphomas and lymphocytic leukaemias; multiple myeloma; myeloproliferative neoplasms, such as chronic myeloid leukaemia and primary myelofibrosis, through extramedullary haematopoiesis; haemolytic anaemias and thalassaemia, through compensatory extramedullary haematopoiesis in severe chronic anaemia.
3. **Congestion from venous outflow obstruction**
   - Intrahepatic disorders retarding portal drainage: cirrhosis (the main cause of massive splenomegaly), schistosomiasis.
   - Extrahepatic obstruction of the portal or splenic vein: portal vein thrombosis, pyelophlebitis (inflammation of the portal vein), thrombosis from tumours of the stomach or pancreas.
   - Systemic venous congestion from right-sided heart failure, as in tricuspid or pulmonic valve disease and chronic cor pulmonale; the spleen rarely exceeds 500 g.
4. **Infiltrative disorders:** sarcoidosis, amyloidosis, glycogen storage diseases, lipid storage diseases and metastases.

## Write down the etiology of hepatocellular carcinoma.

Most hepatocellular carcinomas occur in chronic liver disease with **cirrhosis**; 15% to 20% arise in noncirrhotic livers. Chronic injury, inflammation and hepatocyte regeneration lead to **driver mutations**.

- **Chronic viral hepatitis B and C**; more than 85% of cases occur in Asia and sub-Saharan Africa, where chronic hepatitis B infection is common, with peak incidence in young adults who acquired the virus by maternal-foetal transmission
- **Metabolic diseases:** hereditary haemochromatosis and alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency
- **Alcohol-associated liver disease**
- **MASLD** (metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease), which raises the risk even without cirrhosis
- **Aflatoxin**, a mycotoxin of Aspergillus species that contaminates crops; it acts synergistically with alcohol and hepatitis B
- Cirrhosis from **Wilson disease** [hereditary copper accumulation from ATP7B mutation] and chronic biliary diseases, where the risk is lower but still above the population average
- **Hepatocellular adenoma**, especially with beta-catenin activating mutations, in noncirrhotic liver
- **Dysplastic nodules** in cirrhotic liver

## A 30y male with recent blood transfusion has jaundice, weakness. What are the possible fates/outcomes of such a case?

**Hepatitis B** (parenteral spread, including blood transfusion):

1. **Acute hepatitis followed by recovery and clearance of the virus.** Acute infection is mild or subclinical in nearly two-thirds of adults and is self-limited in most cases.
2. **Acute hepatic failure** with massive liver necrosis: 0.1% to 0.5% of acutely infected individuals, in 4 to 8 weeks.
3. **Chronic hepatitis**, with or without progression to **cirrhosis**: 5% to 10% of infected individuals. The risk is greatest, about 90%, in infants infected at birth, and these individuals are at greatest risk of **hepatocellular carcinoma**.
4. **Asymptomatic "healthy" carrier state.**

**Hepatitis C** (parenteral spread):

- Acute infection is asymptomatic in about 85% of individuals
- **Chronic hepatitis** in 80% to 90% of infected individuals
- About 20% of these progress to **cirrhosis** over 20 to 30 years
- Those who develop cirrhosis are at risk of **hepatocellular carcinoma**

## Same (with anorexia, high SGPT): what is the provisional diagnosis?

**Acute viral hepatitis**, due to **hepatitis B or hepatitis C** acquired through the blood transfusion.

- Both viruses are spread by the parenteral route
- Anorexia and jaundice are symptoms of acute hepatitis B
- Serum transaminases (SGPT is alanine aminotransferase) rise in acute infection
- Hepatitis B is confirmed by **HBsAg**, which appears before symptoms and peaks in acute disease, and by **IgM anti-HBc**
- Hepatitis C is confirmed by **HCV RNA**, detectable in blood for 1 to 3 weeks in acute infection; anti-HCV antibodies appear 3 to 6 weeks after infection and can be negative in early infection

## A 59y male with HBV history has hepatomegaly, SOL in right lobe on USG. How will you proceed to diagnose?

A space-occupying lesion in a man with chronic hepatitis B is **hepatocellular carcinoma** until proved otherwise.

1. **Clinical assessment** for features of underlying chronic liver disease
2. **Serum alpha-fetoprotein:** a frequent finding in advanced disease, but not sensitive as a screening test for early tumours and not seen in the fibrolamellar variant
3. **Ultrasonography**, used to screen high-risk patients such as those with cirrhosis
4. **Contrast-enhanced CT or MRI:** early enhancement of the tumour in the arterial phase, followed by rapid venous washout; these findings are highly characteristic of hepatocellular carcinoma

## Same: what are your expected findings?

**Clinical**

- Abdominal pain, malaise, fatigue, weight loss and hepatomegaly
- Features of underlying chronic liver disease

**Investigations**

- Raised **serum alpha-fetoprotein** in advanced disease
- CT or MRI: early arterial enhancement followed by rapid venous washout

**Morphology**

- Single mass, multiple discrete masses, or diffuse infiltration of the liver, usually in a cirrhotic liver
- Masses may be pale yellow from **fatty change** or green from cholestasis
- Tumours larger than 2 cm are more likely to show vascular invasion and intrahepatic metastases; veins may be invaded, with extension into the portal vein, inferior vena cava and right side of the heart
- Cells resemble normal hepatocytes in well and moderately differentiated tumours, growing in thick plates or trabeculae, pseudoglandular structures with bile plugs, or sheets
- Haematogenous metastases, especially to the lung, occur late; lymph node metastases occur in less than 5% of cases

## Give an outline of the spectrum of liver diseases associated with alcohol abuse.
