There are three distinctive, overlapping forms of alcohol-induced liver injury. Injury begins in centrilobular **zone 3** and extends outward toward the portal tracts as it becomes more severe.

| Form | Pathology | Clinical features |
|---|---|---|
| **Steatosis (fatty change)** | Lipid droplets accumulate in hepatocytes, small at first and then coalescing; mainly macrovesicular. Liver enlarged (4 to 6 kg), soft, yellow and greasy. Completely reversible on abstention | Hepatomegaly with mild rise of serum bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase; severe dysfunction is unusual |
| **Alcohol-associated (alcoholic) steatohepatitis** | **Ballooned hepatocytes** with **Mallory hyaline**; neutrophils around ballooned hepatocytes; lobular lymphocytic infiltrate; spotty necrosis or apoptosis; perivenular and pericellular "chicken wire" **fibrosis** | Acute onset after a bout of heavy drinking: malaise, anorexia, weight loss, upper abdominal discomfort, tender hepatomegaly; raised bilirubin, aminotransferases (**AST higher than ALT by 2:1 or more**) and alkaline phosphatase; neutrophil leucocytosis. Each episode carries a 10% to 20% risk of death |
| **Fibrosis leading to cirrhosis** | Progression from perivenular fibrosis to periportal and bridging fibrosis; micronodular cirrhosis (**Laennec cirrhosis** [micronodular cirrhosis, classically alcoholic], the micronodular cirrhosis of alcohol-associated liver disease) with nodules averaging 3 mm; fat no longer seen in the burned-out stage | Hyperbilirubinaemia, raised aminotransferases, variable alkaline phosphatase, hypoproteinaemia and anaemia; cirrhosis develops in about one-third of patients with repeated bouts of hepatitis within a few years |

- Short-term ingestion of about 80 g of alcohol produces mild, reversible steatosis
- Risk of severe injury becomes significant at 80 g or more of ethanol per day; 160 g or more daily for 10 to 20 years is frequently associated with severe injury
- Only 10% to 15% of patients with alcohol use disorder develop cirrhosis

## What are the factors that favour stone formation within gallbladder?

**Cholesterol stones.** Four conditions contribute:

1. **Supersaturation of bile with cholesterol**, so that cholesterol nucleates into crystals
2. **Hypomotility** of the gallbladder
3. **Accelerated cholesterol crystal nucleation**
4. **Hypersecretion of mucus** in the gallbladder, which traps the crystals

Risk factors:

- **Age and sex:** prevalence rises through life and is higher in females; hypersecretion of biliary cholesterol plays the major role; metabolic syndrome and obesity are associated
- **Oestrogen** (oral contraceptives, pregnancy): increases hepatic lipoprotein receptors and HMG-CoA reductase activity, so biliary cholesterol secretion is increased
- **Obesity and rapid weight loss**, which increase biliary cholesterol secretion
- **Gallbladder stasis**, neurogenic or hormonal
- **Hereditary:** ABC transporter genes, particularly a common variant of the sterol transporter gene ABCG8

**Pigment stones.** Raised unconjugated bilirubin in bile favours stones:

- Chronic **haemolytic anaemia**
- Severe ileal dysfunction or bypass
- **Bacterial contamination of the biliary tree** (Escherichia coli, Ascaris lumbricoides, liver fluke Clonorchis sinensis): microbial beta-glucuronidases hydrolyse bilirubin glucuronides
- Gallbladder stasis

## What are the liver function tests (names, normal values; short note)?

Reference ranges vary between laboratories.

| Group | Test | Normal value | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hepatocellular injury** | **ALT** (alanine aminotransferase) | 4 to 36 IU/L | More liver-specific than AST. In alcohol-associated liver disease AST is higher than ALT by 2:1 or more |
| | **AST** (aspartate aminotransferase) | 5 to 30 IU/L | Less specific; also found in muscle, heart, kidney, brain and blood cells |
| **Cholestasis** | **Alkaline phosphatase** | 30 to 120 IU/L | Not liver-specific; also from bone, placenta and intestine. Raised in cholestatic disease |
| | **GGT** (gamma-glutamyl transpeptidase) | 6 to 50 IU/L | Raised together with alkaline phosphatase in cholestatic disease; shows that a raised alkaline phosphatase is from the liver or biliary tract |
| | **Serum bile acids** | Up to about 10 micromol/L (fasting) | Raised in acute and chronic hepatitis, cirrhosis and cholestasis, including intrahepatic cholestasis of pregnancy; a sensitive marker of impaired hepatic clearance but not specific for the type of liver disease |
| **Bilirubin** | **Total bilirubin** | 2 to 17 micromol/L (0.3 to 1.2 mg/dL) | Jaundice becomes evident above 2 to 2.5 mg/dL |
| | **Direct bilirubin** | 0 to 6 micromol/L | Separates conjugated from unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia |
| **Synthetic function** | **Prothrombin time** | 10.9 to 12.5 seconds | Prolonged when clotting factor synthesis is impaired |
| | **Serum albumin** | 35 to 50 g/L | Slow to change and nonspecific |
| | **Total protein** | 60 to 80 g/L | Albumin plus globulins |

## Name synthetic liver function tests.

1. **Prothrombin time**, which reflects synthesis of the clotting factors (II, V, VII, IX, X, XI, XII and fibrinogen) made by hepatocytes
2. **Serum albumin**

## Name two causes of raised prothrombin time.

1. **Liver failure** (hepatocellular disease): hepatocytes synthesise clotting factors II, V, VII, IX, X, XI and XII and fibrinogen, so factor deficiency and coagulopathy develop
2. **Vitamin K deficiency**, as in cholestasis, where malabsorption causes deficiency of the fat-soluble vitamins A, D and K

## Why is prothrombin time more specific than total protein estimation?

- **Prothrombin time** tests directly the synthesis of clotting factors by hepatocytes; with liver failure these factors fall and coagulopathy develops
- **Total protein** includes globulins, which are raised (hyperglobulinaemia) in chronic hepatitis
- **Albumin** is slow to change and nonspecific; it is also low in poor nutrition, inflammation, and renal or gastrointestinal protein loss

## What is jaundice?

**Jaundice** is yellow discolouration of the skin (jaundice) and conjunctiva (icterus) due to **retention of bilirubin**. It becomes evident when serum bilirubin rises above 2 to 2.5 mg/dL (normal 0.3 to 1.2 mg/dL).

## Classify jaundice.

| Predominant bilirubin | Mechanism | Causes |
|---|---|---|
| **Unconjugated** | Excess production | **Haemolytic anaemia**; resorption of blood from internal haemorrhage (alimentary tract bleeding, haematoma); ineffective erythropoiesis (pernicious anaemia, thalassaemia) |
| | Reduced hepatic uptake | Drug interference with membrane carrier systems; some cases of **Gilbert syndrome** [benign unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia from reduced UGT1A1 activity] (mild inherited deficiency of UGT1A1, the bilirubin-conjugating enzyme) |
| | Impaired conjugation | **Physiologic jaundice of the newborn** (decreased UGT1A1 activity); breast milk jaundice (beta-glucuronidases in milk); genetic UGT1A1 deficiency: **Crigler-Najjar syndrome** [inherited UGT1A1 deficiency with severe unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia] types I and II (inherited UGT1A1 deficiency, severe in type I) and some cases of Gilbert syndrome |
| **Conjugated** | Deficiency of canalicular membrane transporters | **Dubin-Johnson syndrome** [inherited conjugated hyperbilirubinaemia with a black pigmented liver] (MRP2 gene mutation) and **Rotor syndrome** [inherited conjugated hyperbilirubinaemia without liver pigmentation] (inherited conjugated hyperbilirubinaemia); both autosomal recessive and clinically innocuous |
| | Hepatocellular disease | Viral or drug-induced hepatitis, cirrhosis |
| | Impaired bile flow | Duct obstruction; autoimmune cholangiopathies |

## Give the differences between hemolytic and obstructive jaundice.

| Feature | Haemolytic jaundice | Obstructive jaundice |
|---|---|---|
| **Cause** | Excess bilirubin production: haemolytic anaemia, ineffective erythropoiesis | Obstruction of large bile ducts: stones, malignant neoplasms of the biliary tree or head of pancreas, strictures |
| **Bilirubin raised** | **Unconjugated**, usually below 3.5 mg/dL | **Conjugated** |
| **Urine bilirubin** | **Absent**; unconjugated bilirubin is insoluble and cannot be excreted in urine | **Present**, dark urine; conjugated bilirubin is water soluble |
| **Aminotransferases** | Normal | Below 200 U/L |
| **Other findings** | Anaemia, ecchymoses; peripheral blood smear confirms | Raised **alkaline phosphatase and GGT** (more than 3 times normal); **pruritus**, skin xanthomas, steatorrhoea, deficiency of vitamins A, D and K; clay-coloured stools if severe |
| **Complications** | **Kernicterus** in infants, when unbound unconjugated bilirubin enters the brain | **Fibrosis and biliary cirrhosis** if persistent; **ascending cholangitis** |

## What is physiological jaundice?

- **Transient and mild unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia**, nearly universal in the first week of life; it resolves by 2 weeks
- Cause: levels of **UGT1A1** (the enzyme that conjugates bilirubin with glucuronic acid) are low at birth and do not reach adult levels until 3 to 4 months of age
- **Breastfeeding** may exacerbate it, possibly because of bilirubin-deconjugating enzymes in breast milk
- **Phototherapy** with blue light converts bilirubin to a soluble isomer that is excreted in urine, and is sufficient in most infants

## Mention 2 important causes of obstructive jaundice.

1. **Stones in the bile duct** (choledocholithiasis)
2. **Malignant neoplasms** of the biliary tree or the head of the pancreas, usually adenocarcinoma

## What enzymes are elevated in hepatocellular and obstructive jaundice (AST, ALT, ALP interpretation)?

| Type | Enzymes raised | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| **Hepatocellular jaundice** | **AST and ALT** (aminotransferases) | Above 500 U/L; alkaline phosphatase is below 3 times normal. In alcohol-associated liver disease AST is higher than ALT by 2:1 or more; in other chronic liver diseases ALT tends to be higher than AST |
| **Obstructive (cholestatic) jaundice** | **Alkaline phosphatase and GGT** (gamma-glutamyl transpeptidase) | Both more than 3 times normal; they are present on the canalicular membranes of hepatocytes and bile duct epithelial cells and are characteristic of cholestatic disease. Aminotransferases are below 200 U/L |

## Give the urinary findings of obstructive jaundice.

- **Bilirubin present** in the urine, because conjugated bilirubin is water soluble and loosely bound to albumin
- **Dark urine**

## What is acholuric jaundice?

Jaundice in which the urine contains **no bilirubin** because the raised bilirubin is **unconjugated**. Unconjugated bilirubin is largely insoluble and cannot be excreted in the urine. It is seen in **haemolytic anaemia**, where the excess serum bilirubin is unconjugated.

## How will you differentiate hemolytic, hepatocellular and obstructive jaundice biochemically?

| Feature | Haemolytic | Hepatocellular | Obstructive |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Bilirubin raised** | **Unconjugated** | **Conjugated** | **Conjugated** |
| **Urine bilirubin** | **Absent** | Present, dark urine | Present, dark urine |
| **Aminotransferases** | Normal | Above 500 U/L | Below 200 U/L |
| **Other findings** | Anaemia, reticulocytosis; peripheral blood smear confirms | Prolonged prothrombin time or partial thromboplastin time shows hepatic dysfunction | Pruritus, steatorrhoea; clay-coloured stools if severe |

- **Alkaline phosphatase** is below 3 times normal in hepatocellular jaundice
- **Alkaline phosphatase and GGT** are both more than 3 times normal in obstructive jaundice

## How will you investigate a case of hemolytic jaundice?

1. **Serum bilirubin**, fractionated: the excess is unconjugated; jaundice is rarely severe when the liver is normal
2. Complete blood count and peripheral blood smear: anaemia, with prominent reticulocytosis
3. **Bone marrow:** increased numbers of erythroid precursors (normoblasts)
4. **Evidence of haemolysis:** decreased plasma **haptoglobin**; raised lactate dehydrogenase
5. **Direct Coombs test** [antiglobulin test for antibody-coated red cells], positive in immune haemolysis
6. **Urine:** no bilirubin; in intravascular haemolysis, haemoglobinuria and haemosiderinuria, with haemoglobinaemia in the blood
7. **Splenomegaly** on examination in extravascular haemolysis

## Write a short note on obstructive jaundice.

**Definition.** Conjugated hyperbilirubinaemia from impaired bile flow due to obstruction of the large bile ducts; **cholestasis** is retention of bilirubin and other solutes normally eliminated in bile.

**Aetiology**

- Adults: **stones** (choledocholithiasis), **malignant neoplasms** of the biliary tree or head of pancreas (usually adenocarcinoma), strictures after surgery or ischaemic injury, **primary sclerosing cholangitis**
- Children: biliary atresia, cystic fibrosis, choledochal cysts

**Morphology**

- Proximal duct dilatation
- Liver biopsy: portal expansion by oedema, prominent **ductular reaction** at the portal-parenchymal interface, neutrophils around ductules (pericholangitis)
- In ascending cholangitis the neutrophils also involve the bile duct epithelium and lumen
- Cholestasis: green-brown bile pigment plugs in hepatocytes and dilated canaliculi; swollen, foamy hepatocytes (**feathery degeneration**)
- Persistent obstruction: fibrosis and eventually **biliary cirrhosis**

**Clinical features**

- Jaundice, **pruritus**, skin xanthomas
- Malabsorption of fat-soluble vitamins A, D and K
- Raised alkaline phosphatase and GGT
- Dark urine and pale stools

**Complications**

- **Ascending cholangitis** (fever, chills, abdominal pain, jaundice), which can progress to abscess formation, sepsis and death
- Fibrosis and biliary cirrhosis if obstruction persists; the changes are reversible if the obstruction is corrected early

## Write a short note on unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia.

**Definition.** Elevation of serum bilirubin that is predominantly unconjugated (indirect).

**Aetiology**

- Excess production: **haemolytic anaemia**, resorption of blood from internal haemorrhage, ineffective erythropoiesis (pernicious anaemia, thalassaemia)
- Reduced hepatic uptake: drug interference, some cases of Gilbert syndrome [mild inherited UGT1A1 deficiency]
- Impaired conjugation: **physiologic jaundice of the newborn**, breast milk jaundice, genetic UGT1A1 deficiency
- Crigler-Najjar syndrome [inherited UGT1A1 deficiency with severe unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia] type I (severe UGT1A1 deficiency) is fatal around birth; in Crigler-Najjar type II and Gilbert syndrome residual UGT1A1 activity gives a milder picture

**Pathogenesis**

- Unconjugated bilirubin is largely insoluble and cannot be excreted in the urine
- Most is tightly bound to albumin; at excessive levels the unbound fraction rises and may diffuse into tissues, particularly the brain in infants

**Clinical features and complications**

- **Jaundice** becomes evident above 2 to 2.5 mg/dL
- In haemolysis, jaundice is rarely severe when the liver is normal; excess bilirubin excreted in bile often forms **pigment gallstones**
- Neurologic damage in infants: **kernicterus**

## Write a short note on kernicterus.

**Definition.** Damage to the central nervous system caused by unconjugated bilirubin.

**Pathogenesis**

- Haemolysis produces **unconjugated bilirubin**, which passes through the infant's poorly developed blood-brain barrier
- Being water-insoluble, bilirubin binds to lipids in the brain and damages the CNS
- At excessive levels the unbound fraction of unconjugated bilirubin rises and diffuses into the brain
- Occurs particularly in premature infants and with haemolysis, as in immune hydrops fetalis

**Morphology**

- Brain is enlarged and oedematous, with **bright yellow** discolouration from bilirubin deposition
- Most prominent in the **basal ganglia**, also the thalamus, cerebellum, cerebral grey matter, spinal cord and brainstem

**Clinical features**

- Neural damage usually requires a blood bilirubin above 20 mg/dL in term infants; the threshold may be considerably lower in premature infants
- Intense jaundice with signs of neurologic injury

**Treatment**

- **Phototherapy** converts bilirubin to harmless, water-soluble products that are readily excreted; total exchange transfusion in severe cases

## A patient has jaundice, pruritus, bulky pale stool, raised ALP / gallstones with palpable gallbladder. What type of jaundice is this?

**Obstructive (cholestatic) jaundice**, due to obstruction of a large bile duct by a stone or a malignant neoplasm.

- Pruritus and raised alkaline phosphatase are features of cholestasis
- Pale stools and dark urine occur when bile flow is blocked
- Malabsorption and deficiency of fat-soluble vitamins occur in cholestasis

## Classify glomerular disease.

Glomerular diseases are classified as **primary** (the kidney is the only or predominant organ involved) and **secondary** (the glomerulus is involved as one manifestation of a systemic disease).

| Type | Disease | Clinical presentation |
|---|---|---|
| **Primary** | **Postinfectious glomerulonephritis** | Nephritic syndrome |
| | **Crescentic (rapidly progressive) glomerulonephritis** | Nephritic syndrome with rapid loss of renal function |
| | **Membranous nephropathy** | Nephrotic syndrome |
| | **Minimal change disease** | Nephrotic syndrome |
| | **Focal segmental glomerulosclerosis** | Nephrotic syndrome; non-nephrotic proteinuria |
| | **Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis** (type I) | Nephritic or nephrotic syndrome |
| | **Dense deposit disease** (type II membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis) | Haematuria, chronic renal failure |
| | **IgA nephropathy** | Recurrent haematuria or proteinuria |
| **Secondary** | Systemic immunologic diseases such as **SLE** (systemic lupus erythematosus); vascular disorders such as thrombotic angiopathies; metabolic diseases such as **diabetes mellitus**; hereditary conditions such as **Fabry disease** [alpha-galactosidase A deficiency] (a hereditary condition affecting the glomerulus) | Similar to the primary forms |

- Histologic changes are described as **diffuse** (all glomeruli), **global** (whole glomerulus), **focal** (a fraction of glomeruli) or **segmental** (part of each glomerulus)

## Name the primary glomerular diseases.

1. **Postinfectious glomerulonephritis**
2. **Crescentic** (rapidly progressive) glomerulonephritis
3. **Membranous nephropathy**
4. **Minimal change disease**
5. **Focal segmental glomerulosclerosis**
6. **Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis** (type I)
7. **Dense deposit disease** (type II membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis) and C3 glomerulonephritis
8. **IgA nephropathy**
9. **Fibrillary glomerulonephritis**

## What is nephrotic syndrome?

**Nephrotic syndrome**, typically caused by severe dysfunction of the glomerular filter, is characterised by **heavy proteinuria** (more than 3.5 g/day), resulting in **hypoalbuminaemia** and severe oedema, and **hyperlipidaemia** and lipiduria (lipids in the urine).

## What are the causes of nephrotic syndrome?

Nephrotic syndrome is caused by a derangement of the glomerular capillary wall, with increased permeability to plasma proteins. The causes vary with age and geography: in children it is almost always a lesion primary to the kidney, whereas in adults it is often associated with a systemic disease.

1. **Primary glomerular lesions**
   - **Minimal change disease**, the most common in children in North America
   - **Membranous nephropathy**, the most common in older adults
   - **Primary (idiopathic) focal segmental glomerulosclerosis**, at all ages
2. **Systemic causes**, the most frequent being **diabetes**, **amyloidosis** and **SLE**
3. **Less common:** proliferative glomerulonephritides such as **membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis** and **IgA nephropathy**
4. **Secondary membranous nephropathy:** drugs (penicillamine, captopril, gold, NSAIDs) and other systemic diseases; about 75% of membranous nephropathy is primary

## Write a short note on nephrotic syndrome.

**Definition.** Heavy proteinuria (3.5 g or more per day, less in children), hypoalbuminaemia (plasma albumin below 3 g/dL), generalised oedema, and hyperlipidaemia with lipiduria.

**Aetiology.** Minimal change disease, membranous nephropathy and focal segmental glomerulosclerosis; systemic causes (diabetes, amyloidosis, SLE).

**Pathogenesis**

1. Increased permeability of the glomerular capillary wall (endothelium, basement membrane and podocytes act as a size and charge barrier) lets proteins escape, causing **proteinuria**
2. Heavy proteinuria depletes serum albumin faster than the liver can compensate, and increased renal catabolism of albumin also contributes, causing **hypoalbuminaemia**
3. Low plasma albumin lowers intravascular colloid osmotic pressure, causing **generalised oedema**; sodium and water retention aggravates it (aldosterone secretion from renin release, sympathetic stimulation, reduced natriuretic factors)
4. **Hyperlipidaemia** follows from increased hepatic lipoprotein synthesis, abnormal transport of lipid particles and decreased lipid catabolism; lipoproteins leak into the urine as free fat or oval fat bodies (**lipiduria**)

**Clinical features**

- Oedema is soft and pitting, most marked in the periorbital regions and dependent parts; pleural effusions and ascites if severe
- Selectivity of proteinuria: highly selective proteinuria is mostly albumin and transferrin, poorly selective proteinuria includes higher-molecular-weight globulins

**Complications**

- **Infection**, especially staphylococcal and pneumococcal, probably from loss of immunoglobulins in the urine
- **Thrombosis and thromboembolism**, partly from loss of antithrombin III in the urine; renal vein thrombosis is most often a consequence of this hypercoagulable state

## What are the urinary/biochemical findings of nephrotic syndrome?

- **Massive proteinuria:** 3.5 g or more per day (less in children); the largest proportion is albumin. Highly selective proteinuria is mostly low-molecular-weight proteins (albumin, transferrin); poorly selective proteinuria also contains higher-molecular-weight globulins
- **Hypoalbuminaemia:** plasma albumin below 3 g/dL
- **Hyperlipidaemia:** raised cholesterol, triglyceride, very-low-density lipoprotein, low-density lipoprotein, lipoprotein(a) and apoprotein; high-density lipoprotein is decreased in some patients
- **Lipiduria:** lipid in the urine as free fat or as oval fat bodies
- Loss of immunoglobulins and antithrombin III in the urine

## How will you investigate/diagnose a case of nephrotic syndrome?

1. **Urine:** proteinuria of 3.5 g or more per day; selectivity of the proteinuria; lipiduria (free fat, oval fat bodies)
2. **Blood:** plasma albumin below 3 g/dL; serum lipids
3. **Exclude secondary causes:** diabetes, amyloidosis and SLE are the most frequent systemic causes; also underlying malignant tumour, infection (hepatitis B, hepatitis C, syphilis) and drugs, because treating the cause can reverse the injury
4. **Renal biopsy** with light microscopy, immunofluorescence and electron microscopy to identify the lesion:
   - **Minimal change disease:** normal glomeruli on light microscopy; diffuse effacement of podocyte foot processes on electron microscopy, without deposits
   - **Membranous nephropathy:** diffuse thickening of the capillary wall; granular IgG and C3 on immunofluorescence; subepithelial deposits on electron microscopy
   - **Focal segmental glomerulosclerosis:** sclerosis of part of the capillary tuft in a subset of glomeruli

## What is nephritic syndrome (AGN)?

**Nephritic syndrome** is a clinical entity caused by inflammatory glomerular disease and dominated by the acute onset of grossly visible or microscopic **haematuria**, diminished GFR, mild to moderate **proteinuria** and **hypertension**. It is the classic presentation of acute poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis.

## Write down the pathogenesis of acute post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis.

1. Caused by **immune complexes** containing streptococcal antigens and specific antibodies
2. Only certain strains of **group A beta-haemolytic streptococci** are nephritogenic; more than 90% of cases are traced to types 1, 4 and 12, identified by typing of the M protein of the cell wall
3. **Streptococcal pyogenic exotoxin B (SpeB)** is the principal antigen in most cases and is localised to the "humplike" deposits
4. The inciting antigens are first planted from the circulation in **subendothelial** locations of the glomerular capillary wall, leading to in situ formation of immune complexes that elicit an inflammatory response
5. The complexes then dissociate, migrate across the GBM, and reform on the **subepithelial** side; deposition of circulating immune complexes may also contribute
6. Leucocyte infiltration and proliferation of glomerular cells injure the capillary walls, allowing blood into the urine; the fall in GFR causes oliguria, fluid retention and azotaemia
7. Evidence for an immunologic basis:
   - The latent period between infection and nephritis is compatible with the time needed to form antibodies and immune complexes
   - Antistreptococcal antibody titres are raised in the great majority of patients
   - Serum complement is low, from activation and consumption
   - Granular immune deposits are present in the glomeruli

## What are the urinary findings of acute glomerulonephritis?

- **Haematuria:** smoky or cola-coloured urine; **dysmorphic red cells** or **red cell casts**
- **Mild proteinuria**, usually less than 1 g/day
- **Oliguria**

## Mention the complications of acute glomerulonephritis.

- **Acute:** oliguria, fluid retention, azotaemia, hypertension
- **Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis**, in a small minority of children (perhaps less than 1%) who do not improve and become severely oliguric
- **Chronic glomerulonephritis**, with or without recurrence of an active nephritic picture, in some of the remaining patients
- Prolonged and persistent heavy proteinuria and abnormal GFR mark an unfavourable prognosis
- **Adults:** only about 60% of sporadic cases recover promptly; in the rest there is persistent proteinuria, haematuria and hypertension, with progression to chronic glomerulonephritis or rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis in some

## Write the laboratory investigations to differentiate AGN from nephrotic syndrome.

| Feature | Acute glomerulonephritis (nephritic) | Nephrotic syndrome |
|---|---|---|
| **Proteinuria** | Subnephrotic, usually less than 1 g/day | Massive, 3.5 g or more per day |
| **Urine sediment** | Dysmorphic red cells and red cell casts | Lipiduria: free fat and oval fat bodies |
| **Blood** | Raised BUN and serum creatinine (azotaemia) | Plasma albumin below 3 g/dL; raised cholesterol and triglyceride |

- In acute poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis, antistreptococcal antibody titres are raised and the serum concentration of C3 and other complement components is low

## A child has oliguria, cocoa-coloured urine, periorbital puffiness after sore throat/skin infection. What is the probable diagnosis?

**Acute poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis** (nephritic syndrome).

- It usually appears 1 to 4 weeks after a streptococcal infection of the pharynx or skin (impetigo), most often in children 6 to 10 years of age
- The typical child abruptly develops malaise, fever, nausea, oliguria and haematuria (smoky or cola-coloured urine) 1 to 2 weeks after a sore throat
- Periorbital oedema, mild to moderate hypertension, dysmorphic red cells or red cell casts and mild proteinuria are found
- Raised antistreptococcal antibody titres and a low serum C3 support the diagnosis

## List the causes of acute renal failure.

Acute kidney injury can result from glomerular, interstitial, vascular or acute tubular injury.

1. **Acute tubular injury**, the most common cause (about 50% of cases in hospitalised patients):
   - **Ischaemic:** severe trauma, acute pancreatitis, or any period of inadequate blood flow with hypotension and shock
   - **Nephrotoxic:** gentamicin, radiographic contrast agents, heavy metals (mercury), organic solvents (carbon tetrachloride)
   - **Combined:** mismatched blood transfusions and other haemolytic crises causing haemoglobinuria; skeletal muscle injury causing myoglobinuria
2. **Interstitial:**
   - Pyelonephritis
   - **Acute drug-induced interstitial nephritis:** synthetic penicillins (methicillin, ampicillin), rifampin, thiazide diuretics, NSAIDs, allopurinol, cimetidine, immune checkpoint inhibitors
   - **Acute uric acid nephropathy:** precipitation of uric acid crystals in the collecting ducts, as in leukaemias or lymphomas treated with chemotherapy (tumour lysis syndrome)
3. **Glomerular:** rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis
4. **Vascular:** thrombotic microangiopathy (haemolytic uraemic syndrome and thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura)

## A 6y girl has watery stool then cessation of micturition. What is the most likely mechanism?

**Typical (diarrhoea-positive) haemolytic uraemic syndrome:**

1. Intestinal infection with an **E. coli** strain (most commonly O157:H7) that produces **Shiga-like toxin**
2. The toxin activates endothelial cells, which increase leucocyte adhesion molecules and endothelin and decrease nitric oxide production; with cytokines such as TNF it may cause endothelial apoptosis
3. **Endothelial injury** leads to platelet activation, thrombosis and vasoconstriction, producing **thrombotic microangiopathy**
4. Thrombi in the glomerular capillaries and arterioles cause ischaemia and renal failure, with red cell haemolysis and consumptive thrombocytopenia

## Classify tumours of kidney.

| Type | Tumour | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Benign** | **Renal papillary adenoma** | Arises from tubular epithelium; less than 1.5 cm; trisomies 7 and 17 |
| | **Angiomyolipoma** | Vessels, smooth muscle and fat; in 25% to 50% of patients with tuberous sclerosis |
| | **Renal oncocytoma** | About 5% of renal neoplasms; arises from intercalated cells of collecting ducts |
| **Malignant** | **Renal cell carcinoma** | Most common malignant tumour (85% of renal cancers in adults): clear cell, papillary, chromophobe, Xp11 translocation, collecting duct carcinoma |
| | **Wilms tumour** [nephroblastoma, a childhood renal tumour] (nephroblastoma) | Childhood |
| | **Urothelial carcinoma** of the calyces and pelves | 5% to 10% of primary renal tumours |

## Write down the morphology of renal cell carcinoma (short note).

**Gross**

- May arise in any part of the kidney, more commonly the poles
- **Clear cell carcinoma:** usually a solitary unilateral lesion; bright **yellow-grey-white** spherical mass that distorts the kidney; the yellow colour is from lipid in tumour cells
- Large areas of grey-white necrosis and foci of haemorrhage; margins usually sharply defined and confined within the renal capsule
- May bulge into the calyces and pelvis and extend into the ureter
- Strong tendency to invade the **renal vein**, growing as a solid column that may extend up the inferior vena cava to the right side of the heart

**Microscopy**

- **Clear cell:** rounded or polygonal cells with abundant clear or granular cytoplasm containing glycogen and lipid, in nested, tubular, solid or pseudopapillary patterns; delicate branching vasculature; cystic areas; larger tumours show hyalinisation; most are well differentiated, some show bizarre nuclei and giant cells
- **Papillary:** may be multifocal and bilateral; typically haemorrhagic and cystic; cuboidal or low columnar cells in papillae; foamy histiocytes in the papillary cores; psammoma bodies may be present
- **Chromophobe:** pale eosinophilic cells, often with a perinuclear halo, in solid sheets, with the largest cells around blood vessels
- **Collecting duct carcinoma:** irregular channels lined by highly atypical epithelium with a hobnail pattern
- **Sarcomatoid change** is infrequent in all types and carries a very poor prognosis

## Write a short note on Wilms' tumour/nephroblastoma.

**Definition.** **Wilms tumour** [nephroblastoma, a childhood renal tumour] (nephroblastoma) is the most common primary renal tumour of childhood and the fourth most common paediatric malignancy. Peak incidence is 2 to 5 years of age, and 95% occur before 10 years. 5% to 10% involve both kidneys.

**Aetiology and pathogenesis**

| Syndrome | Features | Genetic basis | Risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| **WAGR syndrome** (Wilms tumour, aniridia, genitourinary anomalies, intellectual disability) | Aniridia and genitourinary anomalies | Germline deletion of 11p13, with the WT1 gene | About 33% |
| **Denys-Drash syndrome** [WT1 mutation with nephropathy and Wilms tumour] (gonadal dysgenesis and early-onset nephropathy) | Diffuse mesangial sclerosis leading to renal failure | Dominant-negative missense mutation of WT1 | About 90% |
| **Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome** [overgrowth syndrome with macroglossia and embryonal tumour risk] (organomegaly, macroglossia, hemihypertrophy, omphalocele, adrenal cytomegaly) | Genomic imprinting disorder | 11p15.5 (WT2 locus); loss of imprinting or paternal disomy with overexpression of IGF2 | Increased |

- Sporadic tumours account for 90%: beta-catenin gain-of-function mutations in about 10%; mutations of microRNA processing genes (DROSHA, DGCR8, DICER1) in 15% to 20% of tumours with predominantly blastemal histology
- **TP53** mutation is associated with anaplasia and poor prognosis
- **Nephrogenic rests** are precursor lesions

**Morphology**

- Large, solitary, well-circumscribed mass; soft, homogeneous and tan-grey on cut section, with foci of haemorrhage, cyst formation and necrosis; about 10% are bilateral or multicentric
- Classic **triphasic** pattern: **blastemal** (sheets of small blue cells), **epithelial** (abortive tubules or glomeruli) and **stromal** (fibroblastic or myxoid, sometimes skeletal muscle) components
- About 5% are **anaplastic**: large hyperchromatic pleomorphic nuclei and abnormal mitoses
- Nephrogenic rests are found next to 25% to 40% of unilateral tumours and nearly 100% of bilateral tumours

**Clinical features and complications**

- Large abdominal mass, unilateral or extending across the midline into the pelvis; haematuria, abdominal pain, intestinal obstruction and hypertension; pulmonary metastases may be present at diagnosis
- Cure rate is about 90%; anaplastic histology is the most critical adverse factor
- Increased risk of second primary tumours (sarcomas, leukaemia, lymphoma, breast cancer), some from radiation therapy

## A male has haematuria, loin pain, palpable lumbar mass. What is the likely diagnosis?

**Renal cell carcinoma.**

- The classic features are costovertebral pain, a palpable mass and haematuria, but all three are seen in only 10% of cases
- Haematuria is the most reliable clue, but it is usually intermittent and may be microscopic
- It occurs most often in the sixth and seventh decades, with a 2:1 male preponderance

## Same: what are the histologic types of this lesion?

| Type | Frequency | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Clear cell** | 70% to 80% | Clear or granular cytoplasm in variably sized nests; deletion of chromosome 3 at band p25.3 (the VHL gene site) in 98% |
| **Papillary** | 10% to 15% | Papillary, tubulopapillary or solid growth; trisomies 7 and 17; MET mutations in the familial form |
| **Chromophobe** | 5% | Prominent cell membranes and pale eosinophilic cytoplasm with a perinuclear halo; arises from intercalated cells of collecting ducts; excellent prognosis |
| **Xp11 translocation** (TFE3-rearranged) | Not given | Translocations of the TFE3 gene at Xp11.2; often in young patients |
| **Collecting duct carcinoma** | About 1% or less | Malignant cells forming glands in a desmoplastic stroma in the medulla; medullary carcinoma is a similar tumour in patients with sickle cell trait |
